# PHASE 6, PART 1: SQL and Relational Database Systems

**Dialect convention.** Every example is PostgreSQL unless the text says otherwise. Where MySQL behaves differently the difference is called out in 6.9, because "SQL" is a standard with a small common core and a very long dialect-specific tail. Expected result sets are given exactly: columns in the order written, rows in the order returned **unless the query has no `ORDER BY`**, in which case the row order is genuinely unspecified and the correct answer is a set, not a list. Saying that out loud in an interview is worth more than reciting a lucky plan order.

**Why this phase is weighted the way it is.** Almost every backend interview includes at least one SQL question, most include two or three, and senior rounds add a schema-design, migration, or query-debugging question. SQL is also the one part of the backend stack where a wrong answer is silent: a query that returns the wrong number is worse than a query that fails loudly.

**How to use this part.** 6.1 through 6.10 are the knowledge base. 6.11 exercises, 6.12 trace drills, 6.13 hands-on scenarios, 6.14 Q&A, 6.15 debugging, 6.16 rapid-fire, and 6.17 checklist are the practice surface. Do not read 6.16 before you can write the top-N-per-group and gaps-and-islands queries without looking.

---

# 6.1 SQL Foundations

Beginner to Intermediate · **Must-know**

## 6.1.1 Relational Guarantees and Primary Key Choices

**What a relational table actually promises**

A table is a set of tuples. Four consequences follow, and every SQL surprise later in this phase traces back to one of them:

- **Rows are unordered.** The database is free to return rows in any order. `SELECT ... LIMIT 10` without `ORDER BY` is not "the first ten rows"; it is ten arbitrary rows that may change between two identical queries. If you need stable pagination, you need a deterministic `ORDER BY` *and* a tie-breaker (`ORDER BY created_at, id`), otherwise page 2 can repeat or skip rows.
- **Rows are identified, not addressed.** You find a row by its key, never by "the third row" or by physical location. This is why `OFFSET 1000000` degrades linearly: the engine still has to walk or skip a million index entries.
- **Values are atomic.** A cell holds one indivisible value, not a list. `tags = '{a,b}'` and `profile = '{"x":1}'` violate 1NF at the storage layer even though the engine will happily store them (see 6.8). You can query across them, but you lose the constraints, joins, and per-element statistics that a real table gives you.
- **NULL means unknown, and unknown propagates.** Covered in 6.1.3, and it is the single largest source of "my WHERE clause ignores rows" bugs.

**What the storage engine guarantees beyond the model**

- **Uniqueness and referential integrity are enforced by indexes, not by "convention."** A `PRIMARY KEY` in Postgres is backed by a unique B-tree index; a `FOREIGN KEY` is backed by no index of its own on the child side (you must add one), only a trigger or internal check on the parent side at insert/update time.
- **A single statement is atomic.** A failed multi-row `INSERT` leaves no partial rows. Atomicity *across* statements requires an explicit transaction (6.5).
- **Durability is configurable.** In Postgres, `synchronous_commit = on` (the default) means a `COMMIT` has flushed its WAL to disk before returning; `off` trades the last few milliseconds of committed transactions for throughput after a crash. This is a per-session, per-transaction dial, not a promise the database makes unconditionally.
- **Isolation is `READ COMMITTED` by default in Postgres and `REPEATABLE READ` by default in MySQL.** Same SQL, different guarantees, same words. Read 6.5 before assuming anything.

**Surrogate vs natural primary key**

| Dimension | Surrogate key (`bigint`/`identity`) | Natural key (a real-world attribute) |
|---|---|---|
| Meaning | Carries no business meaning; exists to identify | Is the business identity (SSN, ISBN, email, slug) |
| Insert pattern | Clustered/appended, index-friendly | Random or irregular insert pattern |
| Update cost | Immutable; business attributes can all change | Renaming a department means rewriting the key everywhere it is referenced |
| Merge/replication | Independent sequences collide on insert; needs offset/identity handling | Natural keys let merge replicates dedupe without coordination |
| Storage | 8 bytes for `bigint` | Wide (email 60+ bytes), often needs a hash index or a separate lookup |
| Legibility in logs | `emp_id = 918` means nothing to a human | `emp_id = 'dana@corp.com'` is self-explaining in a slow-query log |
| Real risk | Business rules leak into "which id do I use", and gaps invite enumeration attacks | Business reality leaks into the schema: two people can share a name, a book can get a new ISBN |
| Where it wins | Most OLTP schemas: fewer updates, smaller indexes, simpler joins | Immutable, externally-assigned, globally-unique identifiers (SKU, ISO codes, ISBN-13) |

The honest senior answer: **use a surrogate key for internal relationships and keep the natural key as a `UNIQUE` constraint.** The surrogate key is a local join target; the `UNIQUE` natural key is the integration surface. This gives you small indexes and immutability internally, plus an idempotent upsert path and a merge/dedupe capability externally. The trap in the other direction is defining `PRIMARY KEY (email)` and then discovering that the product supports changing emails: every `UNIQUE(email)` constraint in the codebase becomes a potential dangling reference, and cascading updates on identity columns rewrite hot index pages.

One nuance: a *composite* natural key (`PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, email)`) is often the best of both — the key is a business identity, the row is scoped, and inserts still land near existing tenant rows in the index.

**`bigint` vs `uuid` vs `uuidv7`/`ulid`: the real cost is the B-tree**

All three give you globally unique, collision-free keys. They differ enormously in *write pattern*, because a B-tree is an ordered structure and every insert must land at the correct position.

| Property | `bigint` + sequence | `uuid` (v4, random) | `uuid` v7 (time-ordered) | `ulid` (time-ordered, 128-bit) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Width | 8 bytes | 16 bytes | 16 bytes | 16 bytes binary / 26 chars text |
| Insert position | Monotonic, always the right edge | Uniformly random: 1/65536-ish of inserts land on each leaf page | Monotonic (48-bit ms timestamp prefix), right edge | Monotonic (48-bit ms prefix + random) |
| Page splits and fill factor | Minimal; leaves stay near full | Constant; new tuples land in the middle of existing leaves, splitting them, and steady-state fill settles around 50-70% | Same as `bigint` | Same as `bigint` |
| Effective index size | Baseline | Roughly 1.5x to 2x baseline, because pages are only half full and half the pages hold no live tuple | ~1.3x baseline from the extra 8 bytes per key | ~1.3x, plus 26 bytes if stored as text |
| Cache behaviour | Sequential right-edge append, prefetcher-friendly | Random writes across the whole index extent: no read-ahead, no locality, more shared-buffer misses and more physical reads after eviction | Sequential | Sequential |
| WAL and checkpoint behaviour | Contiguous WAL append | Random page updates raise the number of dirty pages per checkpoint | Contiguous | Contiguous |
| Insert contention | Hot right edge on the last few pages; mitigated by sharding the sequence | Spread out; less contention | Hot right edge, same as `bigint` | Hot right edge |
| HOT updates (Postgres) | Possible when no indexed column changes | Possible, but every page update is a random write | Possible | Possible |
| Randomness benefit | None | None cryptographically you need (122 bits of entropy is plenty; you do not need unpredictability in a PK unless you are issuing unguessable URLs, which is an authorization bug waiting to happen) | 74 random bits: collision probability negligible | 80 random bits |
| Client-generated | No (needs round trip, or DB-side identity) | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Operational annoyance | Gaps, and sequences need care on failover/replication | Fragmentation, `gen_random_uuid()` calls, 16-byte keys everywhere | Bytea/UUID parse overhead in drivers; requires PG 18 for native `uuidv7()`, otherwise generated in the app | String case/collation sensitivity: `A` vs `a` ordering depends on `LC_COLLATE`; store `uuid` and use `gen_random_uuid()`-style casts or a `C` collation |

Concretely: insert 100 million rows with a random-UUID primary key and then read them back in key order. The leaf level is roughly 50,000 pages of live data spread over roughly 100,000 allocated pages. Every scan that should have read 50,000 pages from a sequential layout reads 100,000 pages with random access, and the working set no longer fits in shared buffers, so the "cheap" sequential read turns into 100,000 random buffer misses. With a time-ordered key, the same table reads 50,000 densely packed pages with prefetch working.

The interview-grade nuance: **v4 UUIDs are not wrong, they are just expensive on write-heavy tables, and the cost is invisible until the table is big.** The migration path from v4 to v7 is painful if you already have v4 keys, because primary keys cannot be changed in place. Decide early.

`uuid` also has a storage surprise in Postgres: `uuid` is a 16-byte binary type, so storing it as `text`/`varchar` roughly triples it plus alignment. Use the native type.

**Model answer, Q2 (PK choice):** "Context: a multi-tenant SaaS orders table, roughly 500M rows, high insert rate, and we ingest from three sources. Decision: `bigint` identity as the internal key, because inserts are append-only at the right edge of the B-tree and reads stay sequential, plus `UNIQUE (tenant_id, external_ref)` for idempotent ingestion from the sources. Reasoning: the surrogate key is immutable and narrow, so secondary indexes stay small and no business rule can force a key update; the natural `external_ref` is stable and supplied by the sources, so it gives us conflict-free upserts. Trade-offs: sequences can produce visible gaps, which leaks approximate row counts and breaks assumptions that IDs are contiguous, and we need sequence ownership care if we ever shard. Failure scenario: a support engineer sees order IDs 1000001 and 1000005 and files a bug about the missing order. Alternative: `uuidv7` generated in the application, which removes the round trip and the sequence failover problem at the cost of 8 extra bytes per index entry and slightly worse compression; I would pick that if client-side ID generation were a hard requirement."

**Questions**
1. What does "relational" actually guarantee? (Sets of tuples, no duplicates, no ordering, atomic values, and the four logical guarantees layered on top by the engine.)
2. Surrogate vs natural primary key: when does each win?
3. Why does a random UUID primary key hurt performance? (B-tree page splits, ~50% fill, random page writes, doubled leaf pages, no prefetch.)
4. `bigint` vs `uuid` v4 vs `uuidv7` vs `ulid`: which do you pick for a high-write table, and why?
5. Why does a `LIMIT` without `ORDER BY` return different rows on two identical calls?
6. Is a `FOREIGN KEY` indexed automatically? (No, on the child side you must create it; the parent side already has the PK/unique index.)
7. What is the storage-layer guarantee for a single statement if it fails halfway?
8. What does `synchronous_commit = off` trade away? (The last committed transactions on crash; a per-session dial in Postgres.)
9. What is the default isolation level in Postgres and in MySQL, and why does the same code behave differently?
10. Why is a table not "an array of objects with a name"? (Uniqueness constraints, joins, per-column statistics, partial updates, and referential integrity all require row-level identity.)
11. If a table is `NOT NULL` on the primary key and unique, what else does the PK give you? (A unique B-tree for fast lookup and the ability for other tables to reference it.)
12. When would you deliberately use a composite natural key such as `(tenant_id, email)`?
13. What breaks if you store UUIDs as `varchar(36)` instead of `uuid`? (3x storage, no native ordering, casting pain, bigger indexes.)
14. Should you make `id` visible to clients as an authorization token? (No: sequential IDs are enumerable; authorize explicitly per row, and expose opaque public identifiers if you need links.)

**Model answer, Q3 (random UUID cost):** "Context: an events table keyed by a v4 UUID, 100M rows, and scans that read the table in primary-key order. The B-tree needs every insert to land at its sorted position. A random key lands in the middle of an existing leaf page almost every time, so each page splits in the middle and steady-state fill settles around 50-70% instead of near 100%. That means the live data occupies roughly half the pages it needs, the working set no longer fits in shared buffers, and reads that were sequential become random buffer misses with no prefetch. It also spreads dirty pages over the whole index, which hurts checkpoints, and raises the per-insert cost because each insert touches a cold page instead of appending to the hot right edge. A v7 or ULID key, or a sequence, restores append-right locality. The extra 8 bytes per key is a fair price for halving the number of pages touched."

**Mistakes:** treating rows as ordered; picking `uuid` v4 as the default "best practice" without a workload argument; putting the primary key on a mutable column; forgetting the child side of a foreign key is unindexed; assuming a PK is "just an id"; assuming `LIMIT` without `ORDER BY` is deterministic; assuming a failed multi-statement unit rolls back without an explicit transaction.

**Mini task:** create two tables with identical payloads, one keyed `bigint`, one by random `uuid`; insert 5M rows into each; report `pg_relation_size`, run `VACUUM FULL ANALYZE`, then compare a full-table read in PK order (`SELECT count(*) FROM t` vs an ordered scan) with `EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)`. Then repeat with a v7-style sortable key generated in the app and compare. Record the numbers; the point is to have your own figures rather than the folklore ones.

---

## 6.1.2 Foreign Keys and Referential Actions

A foreign key declares that the value in `child.parent_id` must either be `NULL` (when allowed) or match an existing row in `parent` at insert and at every subsequent update. Enforcement happens on **the child row's write path**: Postgres uses a trigger-ish internal check plus a scan of the parent index (`SELECT 1 FROM parent WHERE pk = $1`), MySQL InnoDB does the equivalent on the child side. Either way, the parent key needs a `PRIMARY KEY` or `UNIQUE` constraint, otherwise the FK cannot be created at all.

```sql
CREATE TABLE dept (
  dept_id   integer GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  dept_name text NOT NULL UNIQUE
);

CREATE TABLE emp (
  emp_id     bigint GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  name       text NOT NULL,
  dept_id    integer REFERENCES dept(dept_id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  salary     numeric(12,2),
  status     text NOT NULL DEFAULT 'active'
    CHECK (status IN ('active','notice','left')),
  hired_on   date NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_DATE
);
```

**The referential action matrix** (Postgres names; `RESTRICT` and `NO ACTION` differ only in *when* the check happens — `NO ACTION` is deferrable by default, `RESTRICT` is not)

| Action | On DELETE of parent row | On UPDATE of parent key | Typical use | Risk |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `NO ACTION` (default) | Error at end of statement (or at commit if deferred) | Error | Strictest; you must handle the children yourself | Surprises people mid-migration |
| `RESTRICT` | Error immediately, cannot be deferred | Error immediately | Same as above, non-deferrable | Use when you truly want "never" |
| `CASCADE` | Delete/update children too | Children follow the new key | Ownership: order line items belong to an order and cannot outlive it | Deletes too much by accident; cascades are invisible in application code |
| `SET NULL` | Set child FK to NULL | Set child FK to NULL | Optional membership: `emp.dept_id` may be unknown | Requires the column to be nullable; silently rewrites many rows |
| `SET DEFAULT` | Set to the column default | Set to the column default | Rarely correct; usually a design smell | Default may not exist or may be invalid |
| `NO ACTION` + `DEFERRABLE INITIALLY DEFERRED` | Checked at `COMMIT`, not per statement | At commit | Cyclic FKs between two tables | A failure surfaces at commit, further from the cause |

**Worked failure examples**

1. **Orphan by deletion.**
```sql
BEGIN;
DELETE FROM dept WHERE dept_id = 2;
-- ERROR: update or delete on table "dept" violates foreign key constraint
--         "emp_dept_id_fkey" on table "emp"
COMMIT;   -- the DELETE is rolled back
```
   With `ON DELETE CASCADE` the same statement deletes the 12 employees first, inside the same transaction. Nothing in the application log says "12 employees vanished" unless you logged it.

2. **Orphan by insertion of a bad parent id.**
```sql
BEGIN;
INSERT INTO emp (name, dept_id) VALUES ('Zed', 999);
-- ERROR: insert or update on table "emp" violates foreign key constraint "emp_dept_id_fkey"
```
   Without the FK you get a row that points at nothing; every report that joins to `dept` silently drops Zed. This is the real reason to have FKs: they convert a silent data bug into a loud error at the boundary.

3. **Cascade deleting the business's core asset by accident.**
```sql
-- 'draft' orders get deleted after 30 days
DELETE FROM orders WHERE status = 'draft' AND created_at < now() - interval '30 days';
-- with ON DELETE CASCADE this also removes order_items, payments, shipments,
-- invoices, and audit rows referencing those orders
```
   If audit rows must survive, `ON DELETE CASCADE` from `orders` is the wrong design: keep the order and soft-delete it (`status = 'deleted'`) instead.

4. **`SET NULL` breaking a `NOT NULL` assumption downstream.**
```sql
ALTER TABLE emp ALTER COLUMN dept_id DROP NOT NULL;  -- prerequisite for ON DELETE SET NULL
ALTER TABLE emp ADD CONSTRAINT emp_dept_fk FOREIGN KEY (dept_id)
  REFERENCES dept(dept_id) ON DELETE SET NULL;
-- now: DELETE FROM dept WHERE dept_id = 2;  => every emp row in dept 2 has dept_id = NULL
-- every query that does INNER JOIN dept silently loses those employees,
-- and every "revenue by department" report loses that revenue
```

5. **Deferrable FK for a cyclic insert.** `employees.manager_id → employees.emp_id` where a new hire and their manager are inserted together: with a non-deferrable FK the second insert fails. Fix with `DEFERRABLE INITIALLY DEFERRED` or by inserting the manager first.

**Migration-safe ordering rules**

- Add the FK with `NOT VALID`, then `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` later: `ALTER TABLE emp ADD CONSTRAINT emp_dept_fk FOREIGN KEY (dept_id) REFERENCES dept(dept_id) NOT VALID;` takes a brief `SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE` lock but does not scan the table; `ALTER TABLE emp VALIDATE CONSTRAINT emp_dept_fk;` takes only `SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE` and reads the table without blocking writes. This is how you add a constraint to a 500M-row table without downtime.
- Adding an FK always needs an index on the child column, else every parent delete does a full scan of the child.
- Dropping an FK is metadata-only and fast; but check for application-level dependencies on the cascade semantics first.

**Model answer, Q3 (cascade risk):** "Context: an e-commerce `orders` table with `order_items`, `payments`, `shipments`, `invoices`, and an append-only `audit_log`. Decision: no cascading delete from `orders` to the money and audit tables; `order_items` cascades (a line item cannot exist without an order) but only for orders that were never authorized. Reasoning: cascades are enforced inside the database, so the effect is invisible at the application layer and unbounded by any business rule the code knows about. Failure scenario: a cleanup job runs `DELETE FROM orders WHERE status='draft' AND created_at < now() - interval '30 days'`; with a blanket `ON DELETE CASCADE` it silently removes historical invoices for those orders, and the finance reconciliation fails a month later with no obvious cause. Trade-offs: keeping orders forever costs storage and slows the `orders` table. Alternative: soft-delete drafts (`status='abandoned'`), let FKs be `NO ACTION`, and purge via an explicit, logged, batched job that a human approved."

**Questions**
1. What exactly does a foreign key enforce, and on which side of the join?
2. Does Postgres create an index for a foreign key? Why does it matter?
3. `NO ACTION` vs `RESTRICT`: what is the actual difference?
4. When is `CASCADE` correct, and what does it cost you in observability?
5. `SET NULL` has a prerequisite. What is it, and how do you prepare for it?
6. How do you add a foreign key to a huge table without downtime?
7. What is a deferrable constraint and which problem does it solve?
8. Why can a self-referencing FK (`manager_id`) be awkward to populate?
9. Your report loses 4% of rows after a delete. What are the likely causes?
10. Should you add an FK between two tables owned by different services? Trade-offs?
11. What happens to `ON DELETE CASCADE` if you later drop the FK constraint?
12. How do you find all FKs pointing at a table before you drop or rename it?

**Model answer, Q4:** "Cascade is correct when the child's existence is meaningless without the parent and the lifetime is identical, for example `order_items` under `orders`, or a `session_tokens` child under `sessions` where tokens never outlive the session. It is wrong when history matters: `payments`, `invoices`, `audit_log`, and anything under a retention policy. The cost in observability is the real reason I hesitate: a cascading delete happens inside the database, so your application audit log records one `DELETE FROM orders` and nothing about the 30 rows that disappeared with it. Compensating is easy when the cascade is explicit code and impossible when it is a schema property. My rule is: cascade only for rows that are worthless without the parent, and never across a table that another team or a regulator reads."

**Mistakes:** forgetting the child index; assuming `NO ACTION` and `RESTRICT` are aliases (timing differs and one is deferrable); using `CASCADE` on anything an auditor reads; forgetting `ON DELETE SET NULL` needs a nullable column; adding a validated FK in one statement on a large table and taking the table offline; deleting parents in application code with a `SELECT` then `DELETE`, which is a race.

**Mini task:** create three tables where `b` references `a` and `c` references `b`, each with a different action, then try six delete scenarios and record the exact error messages. Then add the FK to a table with 10M rows using `NOT VALID` + `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT`, and time both steps while a writer is running.

---

## 6.1.3 NULL Semantics and Three-Valued Logic

SQL's logic is three-valued: `TRUE`, `FALSE`, and `UNKNOWN`. `UNKNOWN` comes from any comparison involving `NULL`, and `WHERE` keeps only rows where the predicate is `TRUE` — `UNKNOWN` is discarded, exactly like `FALSE`.

```
NULL = NULL        -> UNKNOWN        (not TRUE)
NULL <> 1          -> UNKNOWN
NULL IS NULL       -> TRUE           (special syntax, never UNKNOWN)
NULL IS NOT NULL   -> FALSE          (TRUE when the value exists)
NULL + 1           -> NULL
NULL || 'x'        -> NULL           (string concat propagates NULL in Postgres)
TRUE AND UNKNOWN   -> UNKNOWN
FALSE AND UNKNOWN  -> FALSE          (FALSE dominates AND)
TRUE OR UNKNOWN    -> TRUE           (TRUE dominates OR)
NOT UNKNOWN        -> UNKNOWN        (so NOT (a = b) is NOT TRUE and NOT UNKNOWN, never TRUE)
```

`WHERE`, `HAVING`, `JOIN ... ON`, and `CHECK` constraints all use "keep only TRUE" semantics. `CASE` is different: `CASE WHEN cond THEN ...` takes the first branch whose condition is `TRUE`, and falls through to `ELSE` when nothing is `TRUE`.

**The classic wrong turns**

```sql
-- Wrong: returns zero rows, always
SELECT * FROM emp WHERE dept_id = NULL;

-- Wrong: silently empty when any dept_id is NULL
SELECT name FROM emp WHERE dept_id NOT IN (SELECT dept_id FROM dept);

-- Right: two explicit tests
SELECT * FROM emp WHERE dept_id IS NULL;
SELECT name FROM emp WHERE dept_id NOT IN (SELECT dept_id FROM dept WHERE dept_id IS NOT NULL);

-- Right: NULL-safe equality (Postgres and standard SQL)
SELECT * FROM emp WHERE dept_id IS NOT DISTINCT FROM NULL;
```

`IS DISTINCT FROM` is the operator to reach for: `a IS DISTINCT FROM b` is true when they are different **and** neither is NULL; `a IS NOT DISTINCT FROM b` is true when they are equal **or** both are NULL. It is the NULL-safe `<=>` in MySQL. Use it for "find rows whose new value differs from the old, treating NULL as a value":

```sql
-- rows where the department actually changed, counting NULL as a distinct state
SELECT emp_id, name
FROM   emp_new
FULL JOIN emp_old USING (emp_id)
WHERE  dept_id IS DISTINCT FROM dept_old;
```

**`COALESCE`, `NULLIF`, and friends**

| Expression | Meaning | Typical use |
|---|---|---|
| `COALESCE(a, b, c)` | First non-NULL argument; type is the common supertype | Replace missing display values: `COALESCE(nickname, name)` |
| `NULLIF(a, b)` | `NULL` if `a = b`, else `a` | Guard against divide-by-zero: `NULLIF(count, 0)`; stop an accidental empty-string/`-1` sentinel |
| `GREATEST` / `LEAST` | Ignore NULLs in Postgres | `GREATEST(0, x)` clamp. **MySQL returns NULL if any argument is NULL** — a real portability trap |
| `CASE WHEN x IS NULL THEN ...` | Explicit branch | Required when NULL is a meaningful state, not a missing one |
| `IFNULL(a,b)` | MySQL spelling of `COALESCE(a,b)` | Avoid it; use the standard |

**Aggregates and NULL**

```sql
CREATE TABLE score (name text, pts integer);
INSERT INTO score VALUES ('ada', 10), ('bob', NULL), ('cleo', 20), ('dan', NULL);
```

| Query | Result | Why |
|---|---|---|
| `SELECT count(*) FROM score` | `4` | Counts rows |
| `SELECT count(pts) FROM score` | `2` | Counts non-NULL values |
| `SELECT sum(pts) FROM score` | `30` | Ignores NULL |
| `SELECT avg(pts) FROM score` | `15` | Divides by the count of non-NULL values (`30/2`), not by 4. This is usually what people want and never what they assume |
| `SELECT sum(pts)/count(*) FROM score` | `7` | Integer division: `30/4` = 7. Cast: `sum(pts)::numeric / count(*)` = 7.5 |
| `SELECT max(name), min(name) FROM score` | `'dan'`, `'ada'` | NULLs ignored; all-NULL group returns NULL, not an error |
| `SELECT count(DISTINCT dept_id) FROM emp` | ignores NULLs | 3 in the fixture, not 4 |
| `SELECT array_agg(pts) FROM score` | `{10,NULL,20,NULL}` | Aggregates preserve NULLs; `array_agg(pts) FILTER (WHERE pts IS NOT NULL)` = `{10,20}` |

`avg` skipping NULLs is a feature and a trap. For "average score, treating a missing submission as zero" you must write `COALESCE(avg(pts), 0)` for empty groups and `sum(pts) / count(*)` for the whole set — a distinction worth stating in an interview.

**`GROUP BY` and NULL groups**

`NULL` is not equal to `NULL`, but `GROUP BY` and `DISTINCT` treat all NULLs as one group, because grouping is by value-identity, not by the `=` operator:

```sql
SELECT grp, count(*) FROM t GROUP BY grp;
-- grp = 'a' -> 2
-- grp = 'b' -> 2
-- grp = NULL -> 2      <-- all NULL rows land in ONE group
```

**Rules of thumb for NULLs**

1. Decide per column: does "unknown" mean "not applicable" (nullable), "not yet entered" (needs a default), or "explicitly none" (use `'none'` or a sentinel)? Only the first is `NULL`.
2. Keep columns `NOT NULL` by default. `NOT NULL DEFAULT ...` is nearly free; nullable columns infect every query that touches them.
3. Never compare to `NULL` with `=`. Use `IS NULL` / `IS NOT NULL` / `IS DISTINCT FROM`.
4. Guard `NOT IN` subqueries, or use `NOT EXISTS`.
5. Defaults: Postgres 15+ lets you attach a default to an existing column (`ALTER TABLE t ALTER COLUMN c SET DEFAULT 0`), so adding `NOT NULL` with a backfill in one statement became practical.
6. Postgres 15+ also supports `MERGE`, which is the clean way to write upsert-and-update-if-matched logic (see 6.8 for `ON CONFLICT`).

**Questions**
1. Why does `WHERE col = NULL` return nothing?
2. What does `IS DISTINCT FROM` do and when is it the right operator?
3. Why does `NOT IN (subquery)` sometimes return zero rows?
4. `COUNT(*)` vs `COUNT(col)` vs `COUNT(DISTINCT col)` — give the return for the `score` table above.
5. `avg(pts)` returns 15 on the fixture. Why, and what would you write to get 7.5?
6. What is `NULLIF(count, 0)` for?
7. Do all NULL rows form one `GROUP BY` group? Why?
8. `TRUE AND UNKNOWN`, `FALSE AND UNKNOWN`, `TRUE OR UNKNOWN`, `NOT UNKNOWN` — result each time.
9. `CHECK (x > 0)` on a nullable column with `x = NULL`: does it pass?
10. How do you write "changed, where NULL is a value" in a single predicate?
11. Why is `array_agg(col)` dangerous in a `LEFT JOIN` query?
12. `LEAST(x, 0)` with `x = NULL`: what do Postgres and MySQL each return?

**Model answer, Q4:** "`COUNT(*)` counts rows, so it returns 4 on the fixture. `COUNT(pts)` counts non-NULL values in that column, so it returns 2. `COUNT(DISTINCT pts)` would also return 2 here because the two NULLs are one group and the two non-NULL values happen to be distinct. The mental model I use: `COUNT(*)` counts tuples, `COUNT(expr)` counts the non-NULL results of evaluating `expr`, and `COUNT(DISTINCT expr)` counts distinct non-NULL results with all NULLs collapsed into one group that is then excluded. The trap is `avg`: it divides by `COUNT(col)`, not `COUNT(*)`, so `avg(pts)` is 15, not 7.5. When I want missing values to count as zero I say so explicitly with `sum(pts) / count(*)` and a numeric cast, because integer division would otherwise truncate."

**Mistakes:** `= NULL`; unguarded `NOT IN`; assuming `avg` divides by row count; assuming `NOT (a = b)` means `a <> b` (it is UNKNOWN when either is NULL); forgetting to `IS NOT NULL`-guard a nullable FK before a `NOT IN`; relying on MySQL's `LEAST`/`GREATEST` NULL behavior.

**Mini task:** with the `score` table above, run all eight aggregate variants and write the results in a table. Then write one query per aggregation that treats NULL as zero, one that treats NULL as "excluded", and one that errors on NULL, and explain when each is right for a reporting requirement.

---

## 6.1.4 Constraints Beyond NOT NULL

Constraints are the cheapest correctness tool in the database: they are enforced on every writer, including scripts, migrations, admin consoles, and the other team's service. Application validation is necessary but not sufficient.

| Constraint | Guarantees | Enforced when | Cost | Traps |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `NOT NULL` | Column always has a value | On write | None | With a default, no rewrite in Postgres 11+ |
| `CHECK (expr)` | `expr` is TRUE for every row (NULL passes, per SQL) | On insert and update | Re-evaluated per row; becomes a constraint only if it references only that row's columns | Not enforced by old MySQL versions; a volatile function is re-checked every row; `CHECK (col > 0)` allows NULL, so pair it with `NOT NULL` |
| `UNIQUE (cols)` | No two rows share the key | On write; needs a unique index | One extra B-tree; a nullable column makes the row "distinct" from all other NULLs unless a `NULLS NOT DISTINCT` constraint is used (PG 15+) | Two NULLs are allowed by default: `UNIQUE(email)` lets two NULL emails in |
| `PRIMARY KEY` | `NOT NULL` + `UNIQUE` | On write | Index | In Postgres a PK is not auto-created as a separate index if you use `PRIMARY KEY` on a partition... (partition rules in 6.8) |
| `EXCLUDE USING gist` | No two rows' ranges overlap | On write; uses a GiST index | GiST index cost; heavier on insert | Needs a range/GiST-compatible operator class; must be paired with `btree_gist` for the equality columns |
| `GENERATED ... AS (...) STORED` | Derived value always consistent | On write of the inputs | CPU per write; re-computed on `UPDATE` of a source column | Must be immutable; cannot reference another table; requires a rewrite to add to an existing table |
| Domain-ish: `ENUM` (Postgres) / `CHECK (IN (...))` | Value is in an allowed set | On write | Small; `ENUM` labels must be added to be removable (`ALTER TYPE ... ADD VALUE`) | Adding a value to a Postgres `ENUM` cannot run inside a transaction block before PG 12 |
| `DEFERRABLE ... INITIALLY DEFERRED` | Checked at commit | At `COMMIT` | Keeps a pending-trigger queue | Failure surfaces late |

**`EXCLUDE`: preventing overlapping ranges**

This is the constraint most candidates do not know, and it is the correct tool for bookings, reservations, shifts, and version histories.

```sql
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS btree_gist;   -- lets a plain equality column join a range in EXCLUDE

CREATE TABLE booking (
  booking_id  bigint GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  room_id     integer NOT NULL,
  booked_by   integer NOT NULL REFERENCES app_user(user_id),
  during      daterange NOT NULL,
  note        text,
  CONSTRAINT no_double_booking
    EXCLUDE USING gist (room_id WITH =, during WITH &&)
);
```

`&&` is range overlap (touching endpoints do *not* overlap, which is what you want for back-to-back bookings: `[2024-01-01,2024-01-02)` and `[2024-01-02,2024-01-03)` are fine). The `btree_gist` extension supplies the GiST operator class for the plain `integer` equality column, so you can mix equality and range exclusion in one constraint.

Failure:
```sql
BEGIN;
INSERT INTO booking (room_id, booked_by, during) VALUES (5, 42, daterange('2024-03-01','2024-03-05'));
INSERT INTO booking (room_id, booked_by, during) VALUES (5, 43, daterange('2024-03-04','2024-03-06'));
-- ERROR:  conflicting key value violates exclusion constraint "no_double_booking"
-- DETAIL:  Key (room_id, during)=(5,[2024-03-04,2024-03-06)) conflicts with existing key (room_id, during)=(5,[2024-03-01,2024-03-05)).
ROLLBACK;
```

This turns double-booking from a race that a uniqueness check cannot catch into a deterministic error. Note the alternative designs and their trade-offs: an application-level "check then insert" has a race window; a `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` on a `rooms` row serializes bookings per room but needs a row to lock (use a parent `rooms` row, or advisory lock); a materialized exclusion is possible but must be kept correct by triggers.

For version history / temporal tables, `EXCLUDE USING gist (entity_id WITH =, valid_period WITH &&)` is the standard way to guarantee "no two versions of the same row overlap in time" that you never have to think about again.

**`CHECK` constraint traps**

```sql
ALTER TABLE emp ADD CONSTRAINT emp_salary_positive CHECK (salary > 0);
-- salary NULL passes the check (UNKNOWN is not FALSE). Add NOT NULL if that is not intended.
-- A CHECK referencing another table's column is not supported and silently does not do what you meant.
-- CHECK (extracted_year = extract(year from hired_on)) using an IMMUTABLE function is fine;
-- CHECK with a STABLE/volatile function (now()) is rejected or mis-evaluated.
-- In MySQL 8.0.16+ CHECK is enforced; before that it was parsed and ignored.
```

Adding a validated `CHECK` to an existing table scans the whole table under a weaker lock; use `ADD CONSTRAINT ... NOT VALID` then `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` for the same two-step pattern as foreign keys.

**Questions**
1. Rank the constraints by cost on write. Why is `NOT NULL` nearly free?
2. What does `CHECK (x > 0)` do when `x` is NULL?
3. Two rows with `email = NULL` and a `UNIQUE(email)` constraint: allowed?
4. How do you express "no double booking" without application logic?
5. What is `btree_gist` for, and what happens without it?
6. When would a `CHECK` constraint be silently ignored? (Pre-8.0.16 MySQL; a function Postgres considers not immutable.)
7. `DEFERRABLE INITIALLY DEFERRED` — what breaks if you use it on a hot path?
8. Postgres `ENUM` vs a lookup table: when does each win?
9. What does `EXCLUDE` cost on insert, and what index does it need?
10. Why can you not `CHECK` against another table, and what do you do instead?
11. Which constraints can be added with `NOT VALID`?
12. How do you drop a constraint without dropping the data (and why might you want to)?

**Model answer, Q8 (enum vs lookup table):** "Context: an `order.status` column with 6 values today, probably 8 in a year, queried by dashboards that join to a labels table. Decision: start with a `CHECK (status IN (...))` constraint and a lookup table for labels; use a Postgres `ENUM` only if the set is genuinely closed. Reasoning: a lookup table is a relation, so it can carry a sort order, a display label, a colour, and a `is_terminal` flag, and adding a state is an ordinary `INSERT` with no lock on a hot type. An `ENUM` is compact (1 label of storage space, compared with a 4-byte `integer`) and rejects typos without a lookup, but adding a value requires `ALTER TYPE ... ADD VALUE` (which could not run inside a transaction before PG 12) and removing one requires recreating the type with an `ALTER COLUMN ... TYPE` that rewrites the table under a lock. Trade-offs: enum storage savings matter at hundreds of millions of rows; label flexibility does not. Failure scenario: with `ENUM`, a new status requires a DDL migration that takes an `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` lock on a 400M-row table during business hours. Alternative: `status smallint REFERENCES order_status(status_code)` with `NOT VALID` + `VALIDATE`, so adding a state never touches the big table."

**Mistakes:** assuming `UNIQUE` covers NULLs; using `CHECK` alone to enforce NOT NULL; application-level overlap checks; running a validated `ADD CONSTRAINT` on a big table during peak; forgetting `btree_gist` for mixed equality/range `EXCLUDE`; writing a `CHECK` that calls a non-immutable function.

**Mini task:** build a `shift` table with `(worker_id, during)` and an `EXCLUDE` that prevents overlapping shifts, then write the equivalent with a trigger and a `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` on a `worker` row. Insert an overlapping shift into each and compare the error messages. Then time `INSERT` into the `EXCLUDE` version to see the GiST cost.

---

## 6.1.5 Data Types and the Traps

**Money: never float, and prefer the right `numeric`**

```sql
-- Wrong: binary floating point cannot represent 0.1 exactly
SELECT 0.1 + 0.2 = 0.3;                 -- false
SELECT 0.1::float8 + 0.2::float8;       -- 0.30000000000000004

-- Also wrong: money is locale-dependent in display and scale is fixed at 2 digits
-- (fine for 2-decimal currencies, wrong for JPY (0 digits) and KWD/BHD (3 digits))

-- Right: exact decimal arithmetic
SELECT 0.1::numeric + 0.2::numeric = 0.3::numeric;   -- true
price numeric(12,2)
-- numeric(12,2): precision 12 total digits, scale 2 decimal places => max 9999999999.99
```

Choose deliberately:
- `numeric(p,s)` — exact, slower than integers, `numeric` without precision is variable-length and slower still. Division produces extra digits (`numeric` division scales to at least 16 significant digits), so round explicitly.
- `bigint` minor units (store cents) — fastest, no decimals, but you carry rounding policy in every service and every report, and multi-currency needs an explicit currency column anyway.
- `money` — display-formatted, locale-sensitive, do not use it as an interchange type.
- `double precision` — for quantities, ratios, and anything that came from a float sensor. Never for money.

Store the currency explicitly (`currency char(3)` or an FK to a currency table) whenever more than one currency is possible, and state that money in a ledger should be integers of the minor unit with a separate FX-rate table.

**Timestamps: `timestamptz` is the only column you should write**

```sql
CREATE TABLE event_log (
  at      timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),   -- stored as UTC microseconds since 2000-01-01
  local   timestamp                              -- no zone: only a wall-clock reading
);
```

- `timestamptz` (also spelled `timestamp with time zone`) stores an absolute instant. Display formatting is the client's job. It does not store your timezone; `AT TIME ZONE` is a display operation.
- `timestamp` (without time zone) stores a wall-clock reading with no instant attached. It is correct only for genuinely zoneless data: a store's opening hours, a recurring schedule, a month label. Using it for events means the row's meaning depends on the session `TimeZone` setting, and it is wrong twice a year if you assume it is UTC.
- Postgres accepts many input aliases, which is how ambiguity gets in: `timestamptz` parses `'2024-03-10 02:00'` in the **session** time zone, and silently changes meaning when you change `SET TIME ZONE`.
- DST edge: a local wall clock may not exist (`2024-03-10 02:30` in `America/New_York`) or may be ambiguous (`2024-11-03 01:30` happens twice). Schedule recurring jobs in UTC, or use an `intraday` range type plus a `timezone` name.
- `date` for calendar dates with no time; `interval` for durations, which Postgres handles correctly across months and DST (`interval '1 month'` + `'2024-01-31'` = `2024-02-29`).
- Postgres precision is microseconds, not nanoseconds. MySQL `DATETIME(6)` is microseconds too; `TIMESTAMP` in MySQL is stored as UTC and converted by the session time zone.

```sql
-- Truncation, not rounding: this is what dashboards need
date_trunc('day', at)                        -- 2024-03-10 00:00:00+00
date_trunc('month', at)
-- Correct week bucketing (ISO weeks start Monday); a bare date_trunc('week') starts Monday too,
-- but date_trunc('week') is ISO-8601 and 4/1-4/6 of a year fall into the previous year's week 53:
SELECT date_trunc('week', at)                -- safe, ISO
```

**Text: unbounded `varchar` is a design decision**

- Postgres `varchar(n)` and `varchar` (no n) are the same storage; Postgres does not enforce the length, it truncates on cast. MySQL enforces it, and exceeding it in strict mode is an error.
- Prefer the specific type when one exists: `text`, `char(n)` (blank-padded, almost always wrong), `citext` (case-insensitive), `inet`/`cidr`, `macaddr`, `jsonb`, `uuid`, `bytea`.
- Unbounded `text` in Postgres is fine and cannot be "too long", so the real discipline is application-level validation plus a `CHECK` for the cases that matter (`CHECK (char_length(email) <= 320)`).
- `character varying` with a length in MySQL is also what indexes nicely under `utf8mb4` — a 255-character `VARCHAR` is up to 1020 bytes, and an InnoDB index key limit is 3072 bytes with `DYNAMIC` row format, so long indexed varchars can silently force prefix indexes or fail.

**Other type traps worth naming in an interview**

| Trap | Detail |
|---|---|
| Integer overflow on `COUNT` and sums | `count(*)` returns `bigint`; `sum(int)` returns `bigint` but `sum(int)` can still overflow; cast before arithmetic: `sum(amount)::numeric` |
| Integer division | `7 / 2 = 3` in both engines; `7 / 2.0 = 3.5`, `7::numeric / 2 = 3.5` |
| Boolean vs integer | Postgres `boolean` is not an integer; `WHERE flag = 1` fails. MySQL `TINYINT(1)` is an integer with a naming convention |
| `serial` vs identity | `serial` is a legacy convenience that creates a sequence and a default; `GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITY` is the standard and lets you `OVERRIDING SYSTEM VALUE` to import explicit IDs. Both leave gaps on rollback |
| `ENUM` ordering | Postgres `ENUM` sorts by definition order, which is convenient and confusing when you reorder labels |
| Empty string vs NULL | In Postgres `'' <> NULL` and they are different states; MySQL treats them as comparable. Decide what `''` means for a text field |
| Trailing spaces | MySQL's `PAD SPACE` collations ignore trailing spaces in comparisons, so `'a' = 'a '` is true; Postgres does not. Collation also decides whether `'A' < 'a'`, which silently breaks case-insensitive sorts and `ORDER BY ... <` range logic |
| `citext` | Case-insensitive comparison that does not use a functional index, but it is not collation-aware for accents: use `unaccent` plus `lower()` for true search behaviour |
| Float equality | Never compare floats with `=`; use a tolerance or an exact decimal representation |
| Time zone in `CURRENT_DATE` | `CURRENT_DATE` and `now()` use the session time zone; a report bucketed by local day changes meaning per client. Set the session zone explicitly in jobs |

**Model answer, Q1 (money type):** "Context: a multi-currency marketplace storing EUR, USD, and JPY, with 200M order lines. Decision: `amount_minor bigint` plus `currency char(3)` (or a `currency_code` FK), and a separate `fx_rate` table with `rate numeric(20,10)`. Reasoning: integers are exact and fast, JPY has no minor unit so a fixed 2-decimal `numeric(12,2)` would be actively wrong for it, and floats cannot represent 0.1 exactly, so a sum over 200M rows accumulates visible error. If the product is single-currency with 2 decimals, `numeric(12,2)` is simpler and perfectly defensible. Trade-offs: minor units push the decimal point convention into every service and report, and require a documented rounding rule per operation; `numeric` is slower and larger in memory. Failure scenario: with `float8`, the daily payout report disagrees with the invoice total by 0.01 and finance cannot reconcile which number is authoritative. Alternative: an append-only double-entry ledger in minor units, with the display amount computed at the edge, so every figure is derived from integer postings that must balance."

**Questions**
1. Why is `float8` wrong for money and what exactly goes wrong?
2. `numeric` vs `money` vs minor-unit `bigint` for a multi-currency product?
3. What does `timestamptz` store, and does it store your timezone?
4. When is a plain `timestamp` the correct type?
5. What happens to `'2024-03-10 02:00:00'` in `America/New_York`?
6. `date_trunc('month', ts)` vs `EXTRACT(MONTH FROM ts)` for a monthly report: what differs?
7. Why is integer division a bug in reporting queries?
8. `varchar(50)` in Postgres: is the limit enforced?
9. What is `identity` over `serial`, and why does it matter for imports?
10. Why do sequences have gaps, and which of your guarantees depend on IDs being contiguous?
11. Collation: what breaks if your DB collation is case-insensitive?
12. `sum(int)` vs `count(*)` return types and overflow behaviour?
13. When do you store an epoch integer instead of a timestamp?
14. What is the failure mode of storing a local wall-clock time for an event?

**Model answer, Q4:** "A plain `timestamp` is correct when the value has no instant attached, and only then. Store `date` for a calendar date, `timestamp` for a zoneless wall-clock reading like a store's opening hours or a recurring schedule, and `timestamptz` for everything that happened at a moment. The reason `timestamp` is dangerous for events is that its interpretation depends on the session `TimeZone`, so the same row means different instants depending on who reads it, and it collides with DST: `'2024-03-10 02:30'` does not exist in `America/New_York` and `'2024-11-03 01:30'` happens twice, both of which Postgres resolves silently. For anything that represents an event, `timestamptz` plus a conversion at the presentation layer removes the whole class of bug. The exception people forget is a recurring schedule that must stay at 09:00 local through DST: that is genuinely zoneless, so it belongs in `timestamp` (or `intraday` plus a zone name) and must not be converted."

**Mistakes:** `float8` for money; `timestamp` for events; assuming Postgres enforces `varchar(n)`; relying on IDs being contiguous; assuming `avg(int)` gives what you think when NULLs exist; mixing time zones between app and DB (`now()` vs the app clock); case-sensitivity assumptions about collation and uniqueness.

**Mini task:** create a table with `amount numeric(12,2)`, `amount float8`, and `amount_minor bigint`; insert 0.1 repeatedly 1,000 times and sum each column; then insert a NULL and compare `count`, `sum`, `avg`; finally run `SHOW TimeZone` and re-run a `timestamptz` query under two different session zones to prove the instant is stable while the display changes.

---

# 6.2 Normalization and Denormalization

Intermediate · **Must-know** (normalization) and **Good-to-know** (when to stop)

## 6.2.1 Functional Dependencies and the Normal Forms

**Definitions first, because every interview argument needs them.**

- A **functional dependency** `X → Y` over relation `R(X, Y, Z)` means: for any two rows of `R`, if they agree on `X` they agree on `Y`. It is a statement about the *meaning* of the data, not about the current rows. `emp_id → name, salary` holds. `name → salary` does not (two people named Chris).
- **Candidate key**: a minimal superkey (no proper subset is a superkey). **Primary key**: the candidate key you chose.
- **Prime attribute**: appears in *some* candidate key. **Non-prime attribute**: appears in no candidate key.
- **Superkey**: any set of attributes whose closure (`X⁺`) includes all attributes of `R`.

| Normal form | Formal requirement | Plain meaning |
|---|---|---|
| 1NF | Every attribute value is atomic and single-valued; rows are identified | No repeating groups, no lists or nested structures inside a cell |
| 2NF | 1NF and every non-prime attribute is fully functionally dependent on *every* candidate key (not just part of one) | Only violated when there are composite keys: no column depends on just part of the key |
| 3NF | 2NF and for every `X → Y` with `Y` not a subset of `X`, `X` is a superkey or `Y` is prime | No transitive dependency: a non-key column may not depend on another non-key column |
| BCNF | For every `X → Y` with `Y` not a subset of `X`, `X` is a superkey | Stricter 3NF: every determinant is a candidate key |
| 4NF | No non-trivial multi-valued dependency | A row cannot have two independent repeating groups: if `(a,b)` and `(a,c)` exist, `(a,b,c)` must exist |
| 5NF/6NF (DJNF) | No join dependency beyond the keys | Only reachable in exotic schemas; mention as awareness |

Lossless-join and dependency-preservation are the two properties that matter in practice: a decomposition is acceptable if you can rejoin without creating spurious rows, and if all the original constraints can still be checked with local constraints. 3NF decomposition from a 2NF fix is always lossless when the determinant becomes a key.

**Worked violations and fixes, with real DDL**

*Bad schema (a classic):*
```sql
CREATE TABLE emp_project (
  emp_id       integer NOT NULL,
  project_id   integer NOT NULL,
  emp_name     text,          -- depends on emp_id only
  project_name text,          -- depends on project_id only
  hours         numeric(8,2),
  hourly_rate   numeric(8,2), -- depends on emp_id only
  budget        numeric(14,2),-- depends on project_id only
  PRIMARY KEY (emp_id, project_id)
);
```

| Normal form violated | Violation | Symptom |
|---|---|---|
| 1NF | Assume instead: `skills text` with `'java,sql'`, or `managers integer[]` | No per-element constraint, no per-element join, no statistics per element |
| 2NF | `emp_name`, `hourly_rate` depend on `emp_id` (part of the composite key only); `project_name`, `budget` depend on `project_id` | Renaming a project rewrites one row per employee; two employees on one project store the budget twice and can disagree |
| 3NF / BCNF | If instead `emp_name → hourly_rate` (a rate that depends on the name), that is a transitive dependency `emp_id → emp_name → hourly_rate` | Changing a rate requires touching rows that share a name |

*Fix (decomposition to 3NF/BCNF):*
```sql
CREATE TABLE emp (
  emp_id     integer PRIMARY KEY,
  emp_name   text NOT NULL,
  hourly_rate numeric(8,2) NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE project (
  project_id   integer PRIMARY KEY,
  project_name text NOT NULL,
  budget       numeric(14,2) NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE emp_project (
  emp_id     integer NOT NULL REFERENCES emp(emp_id),
  project_id integer NOT NULL REFERENCES project(project_id),
  hours      numeric(8,2) NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 CHECK (hours >= 0),
  PRIMARY KEY (emp_id, project_id)      -- the intersection table: a natural 3NF relation
);
CREATE INDEX emp_project_project_idx ON emp_project (project_id, emp_id);
```

**Anomalies, worked through on the bad schema**

| Anomaly | On the denormalized table | On the normalized tables |
|---|---|---|
| **Update** | Ada gets a raise: `UPDATE emp_project SET hourly_rate = 95 WHERE emp_id = 1`. If she is on 3 projects, you must update 3 rows, and if one is missed the data is now contradictory with no constraint to catch it. Or you write `WHERE emp_name = 'Ada'` and update someone else's row | `UPDATE emp SET hourly_rate = 95 WHERE emp_id = 1` — one row, one statement, correct by construction |
| **Insert** | A new employee with no project yet: you have no `project_id`, so you cannot insert the row, or you must invent one (a "no project" project row). A new project with no employees has the same problem | `INSERT INTO emp ...` succeeds immediately; the intersection table simply has no row for them |
| **Delete** | Ada is the only person on project 7. `DELETE FROM emp_project WHERE emp_id = 1` silently destroys the project's name and budget. Or you keep the row and now have a "ghost" employee with no employee record | `DELETE FROM emp WHERE emp_id = 1` leaves the project intact; nothing else is lost |
| **Spurious combinations** | A row claiming Bob works on project 7 for 0 hours may appear by accident; nothing prevents nonsense pairings | Impossible to express: `emp_project` rows assert exactly one thing |

**Common-sense denormalization rules:** 3NF is the target, not BCNF pedantry, for most OLTP schemas; a `status` label repeated in three tables is usually fine to *read* redundantly if one column is the source of truth; the moment you write the same fact twice, name the second one a cache and say who rebuilds it.

**4NF in one example**

```sql
-- 4NF violation: two independent repeating groups in one row
CREATE TABLE emp_awards (emp_id int, award text, skill text, PRIMARY KEY (emp_id, award, skill));
-- (1,'Best Engineer','SQL') and (1,'Best Engineer','Go') force the row
-- (1,'Best Engineer','Go') to exist, which is false.
-- Fix: two tables, emp_award(emp_id, award) and emp_skill(emp_id, skill)
```

**Model answer, Q6 (when 3NF is not enough):** "Context: an order page that reads orders, customers, and shipping addresses, then sorts by `created_at`. The 3NF schema requires three joins; on 200M orders that join is the page's whole latency budget, and the read is on the hot path for every customer. Decision: keep 3NF as the source of truth for writes and add a read-optimized projection, either a materialized view refreshed incrementally, or a denormalized `order_listing` table written by the same transaction, or a JSONB `summary` column on the order maintained by a trigger, depending on how much query variety there is. Reasoning: the join cost and the coupling are the problem, and both are solved by making the read shape fixed. Trade-offs: the copy can drift, so every denormalized field needs an owner and a rebuild path, and writes get slightly more expensive. Failure scenario: a backfill updates `orders.shipping_city` directly in a migration script, the denormalized table keeps the old value, and support reports that the address on the order page is wrong with no obvious cause. Alternative: keep it 3NF and add the indexes plus a covering index so the join is index-only, which often removes the need for denormalization entirely."

**Questions**
1. Define 1NF through BCNF in one sentence each.
2. What is a non-prime attribute and why does 3NF care?
3. Give a schema that is 2NF but not 3NF, with the violating dependency.
4. Give a schema that is 3NF but not BCNF. (Classroom example: `R(Student, Subject, Teacher)` with keys `{Student,Subject}` and `{Student,Teacher}` and dependency `Subject → Teacher`.)
5. What does "lossless join" mean and when is a decomposition lossy?
6. Dependency preservation: what is it, and why does it matter for application code?
7. Work through an update anomaly on a denormalized invoice table.
8. Why does an intersection table resolve 2NF for many-to-many?
9. What is 4NF and what does a 4NF violation look like in practice?
10. Does 3NF eliminate redundancy entirely? (No: two identical rows with different surrogate keys still store the same facts; 3NF removes *functional* redundancy, not coincidental duplicates. Say this — it is a strong signal.)
11. When is 2NF enough? (Single-attribute keys, where 2NF and 3NF coincide on the key's own attributes; or genuinely non-transitive data.)
12. How would you reverse-engineer the normal form of an existing messy schema?

**Mistakes:** reciting definitions without an example; treating "normalize everything" as advice on a read-heavy OLAP schema; confusing 3NF and BCNF; claiming 3NF removes all duplicate data; normalizing an audit log that should be append-only.

**Mini task:** take a real table from a project (or invent one with orders, customers, addresses, and line items), draw the attribute-level FDs, decompose to 3NF with DDL, then show the same data stored denormalized and produce one query per anomaly showing the damage. Explain which version you would ship for writes and which for reads.

---

## 6.2.2 Denormalization That Stays Consistent

Denormalization is not a sin; *unowned* denormalization is. Every redundant copy needs four things: a write path, a read path, a rebuild path, and a monitoring signal for drift.

**The four approaches, with trade-offs**

| Approach | How the copy is maintained | Consistency window | Cost | When it fits |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Write in the same transaction | Application writes both tables in one `BEGIN`/`COMMIT` | None (atomic) | Doubles the write cost; both writes must succeed or both roll back | The default when you choose to denormalize. Simple to reason about, and it makes the copy part of the contract |
| Trigger | `AFTER INSERT OR UPDATE` trigger on the source writes the copy | None (same transaction) | Silent write amplification; the copy's logic is invisible in application code; hard to debug; breaks logical replication | Legacy schemas and constraints too complex for app code. Use sparingly |
| Materialized view | `CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW ... AS SELECT ...`; refreshed with `REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW` (`CONCURRENTLY` in Postgres, needs a `UNIQUE` index) | Up to the refresh interval | Double storage; stale reads; cannot be written to | Dashboards, aggregates, expensive multi-table joins. `CONCURRENTLY` in PG 9.4+ refreshes without blocking readers but cannot run inside a transaction and takes longer |
| Periodic ETL / CDC | Debezium or batch job copies changes | Bounded by lag; monitor it | Most moving parts, but fully decoupled and replayable | Cross-service and cross-database copies where a transactional dual write is impossible |

**The dual-write problem.** Two writes in one transaction are atomic only if they are in the *same database*. Once one write goes to a search index, a cache, or another service, you no longer have a transaction, and you need the outbox pattern:

```sql
-- 1. business write + outbox row, same transaction, same database
BEGIN;
INSERT INTO orders (order_id, customer_id, total, status) VALUES (1, 42, 99.00, 'paid');
INSERT INTO outbox (topic, aggregate_id, payload)
VALUES ('order.paid', 1, '{"total":99.00,"customer_id":42}');
COMMIT;
-- 2. a relay reads outbox with FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED, publishes, marks sent
-- 3. consumers are idempotent, because at-least-once is the only guarantee you get
```

This is the single most important thing to say about consistency in a senior answer: **you do not get distributed transactions across services; you get an outbox, idempotent consumers, and a replayable log.**

**Drift detection, concretely**
```sql
-- rows where the copy disagrees with the source
SELECT o.order_id
FROM   orders o
JOIN   order_summary s USING (order_id)
WHERE  o.total IS DISTINCT FROM s.total
   OR  o.status IS DISTINCT FROM s.status;
-- run it on a schedule; alert on a non-zero count
```

**Model answer, Q7 (denormalization):** "Context: a products page needs product name, category name, brand name, rating average, and review count, and it is served on every request for 90% of traffic. The normalized version is six joins and four aggregate subqueries. Decision: denormalize into a `product_card` table written in the same transaction as the product mutation, and additionally expose a `product_card` API that only that table serves. Reasoning: because the read shape is fixed, a single narrow row beats a join plan; and because both writes are in one Postgres transaction, the copy is never inconsistent even if the transaction rolls back, because the copy rolls back too. Trade-offs: writes cost more, so a bulk re-import must use a single `INSERT ... SELECT` rather than row-by-row application code, and the copy must be rebuilt with an explicit script when someone repairs data by hand. Failure scenario: an engineer fixes a bad total with a direct `UPDATE orders` in a hotfix, the summary table keeps the old number, and revenue reporting is off by 4% with no error anywhere. Prevention: a nightly drift check that alerts, and a rule that all writes go through the application layer so this class of manual fix is visible."

**Questions**
1. How do you keep a denormalized copy consistent? List the options and their consistency windows.
2. Why is the outbox pattern necessary?
3. When is a trigger the right tool for a redundant copy?
4. What does `REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW CONCURRENTLY` require and what does it buy?
5. What breaks if a materialized view depends on a table you are migrating?
6. How do you detect drift between a copy and its source?
7. When is caching-aside a better denormalization than a table copy?
8. How do you bulk-rebuild a denormalized table without downtime?
9. What is the read-repair pattern and when do you need it?
10. How do you decide if the extra write cost is acceptable? (Measure it: compare `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT` throughput with and without the copy.)

**Mistakes:** assuming the application always writes both sides; calling a materialized view "always fresh"; forgetting that triggers do not fire on `TRUNCATE` and usually not on bulk operations in some engines; double-counting revenue because a copy and a source are both summed; putting a denormalized copy in a *different* database and assuming ACID covers it.

**Mini task:** build `orders` plus `order_summary` written in one transaction, then deliberately break it (update only `orders` in psql), and write the drift query. Then convert the copy to a materialized view and to a trigger-maintained column, and compare consistency behaviour and write latency for 1M `INSERT`s.

---

## 6.2.3 Join Tables vs JSONB vs Arrays

The decision is not "relational good, JSON bad". It is about **who queries the data, how often, and what must be guaranteed about each element**.

| Aspect | Join table | JSONB (or `json`/`array`) |
|---|---|---|
| Referential integrity | Full: `NOT NULL` + FK on each element | None: a deleted category can remain inside the array forever |
| Per-element uniqueness | `UNIQUE (product_id, tag)` | Must be enforced in application code; `jsonb` has no element-level unique index without an expression index |
| Element-level constraints | `CHECK` per column | `jsonb_path_query` checks are not constraints; you can add a `CHECK` on the whole document |
| Querying "rows having tag X" | `WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM product_tag WHERE tag = $1)` with a GIN/hash-free B-tree on `tag` | `WHERE doc @> '{"tags":["x"]}'` with a GIN index on `doc` |
| Ordering and pagination over elements | Trivial: it's a row | Awkward: `jsonb_array_elements` explodes rows; you need `WITH ORDINALITY` and a lateral to rebuild position |
| Aggregate over elements | `sum(qty)` in SQL, indexable | `jsonb_array_elements` + `sum()`, a full unnest per row |
| Cardinality and statistics | Optimizer knows per-column selectivity | GIN on a large document has coarse, fixed selectivity estimates, so plans get worse with more distinct keys |
| Update cost | `DELETE`+`INSERT` the one row, or a targeted `UPDATE` | Rewrite the whole document (or use `jsonb_set`), so a 2MB audit document rewrites for a 20-byte change |
| Size overhead | ~24-40 bytes per row header plus index entries | Compact for sparse keys; Postgres stores `jsonb` with a binary tree layout and dedupes keys, `json` keeps the raw text |
| Row width limit | Bounded by 8KB page concerns only per row | A `jsonb` value can be TOASTed to a few GB; a 20MB document makes `SELECT` on that row read 20MB from TOAST |
| Portability | Standard SQL | Postgres-specific; MySQL `JSON` has its own semantics, and both engines' path-query languages differ |
| Best for | Anything with identity, joins, constraints, aggregation, or reporting | Sparse, read-whole, write-whole, per-request-shaped payloads; opaque upstream blobs; features in flux |

**Concrete comparison for "products with tags":**

```sql
-- JSONB version
CREATE TABLE product (
  product_id bigint GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  name text NOT NULL,
  tags jsonb NOT NULL DEFAULT '[]'
);
INSERT INTO product (name, tags) VALUES ('Widget', '["sale","blue"]'), ('Gadget', '["blue","new"]');

CREATE INDEX product_tags_gin ON product USING gin (tags);
SELECT name FROM product WHERE tags @> '["blue"]';
-- Widget, Gadget

-- Join-table version
CREATE TABLE tag (tag_id integer GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY, name text NOT NULL UNIQUE);
CREATE TABLE product_tag (
  product_id bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES product(product_id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  tag_id    integer NOT NULL REFERENCES tag(tag_id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  PRIMARY KEY (product_id, tag_id)
);
CREATE INDEX product_tag_tag_idx ON product_tag (tag_id, product_id);
SELECT p.name
FROM   product p
JOIN   product_tag pt ON pt.product_id = p.product_id
JOIN   tag t ON t.tag_id = pt.tag_id
WHERE  t.name = 'blue';
-- Widget, Gadget
```

The join-table version gets index-assisted count estimates, `COUNT(DISTINCT ...)`, "products with both tags", referential cleanup on tag deletion, and a per-tag row count for free. The JSONB version is one fewer table, one fewer join, and one fewer round trip — and it happily stores a tag that no longer exists.

**A tell for which one you are looking at:** if any question about the data requires *counting, filtering, or constraining inside* the collection, it belongs in rows. If the data is only ever displayed as a unit, a document is fine.

**`json` vs `jsonb`:** `json` stores the input text verbatim (fast to write, preserves key order and duplicates, no operators beyond cast), `jsonb` parses into a binary form (dedupes keys, drops key order and insignificant whitespace, supports `?`, `?|`, `?&`, `@>`, `<@`, `@@`, `jsonb_set`, indexing, and GIN). Default to `jsonb` unless you must round-trip the exact text.

**Array-specific notes:** arrays are faster and smaller than a join table for pure containment checks (`WHERE tags @> ARRAY['sale']`), and `int[]` with a GIN index is a reasonable "list of ids" shortcut when you never need to query the *referenced* rows. Arrays do not guarantee the referenced rows exist, do not carry their own labels, and make "who references this tag" queries impossible without an unnest.

**Mistakes:** putting high-cardinality, queried data in JSONB; storing an array of ids that silently becomes stale; mixing `json` and `jsonb` in the same project; assuming a GIN index makes JSONB queries as cheap as an equality lookup (GIN on `@>` is fast but returns bitmap scans and, for large documents, lossy rechecks); rewriting a wide document to change one key.

**Mini task:** store 100K products with 0-8 tags each in both shapes, add the right index to each, and time: lookup by single tag, lookup by two tags together, count per tag, delete a tag globally, and update one tag on one product. The last two are where the shapes diverge sharply.

---

# 6.3 Joins

Beginner to Intermediate · **Must-know**

## 6.3.1 The Five Join Types, With Row Counts

Assume this fixture, which is reused by the exercises in 6.11:

```sql
CREATE TABLE dept (
  dept_id   integer PRIMARY KEY,
  dept_name text NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE emp (
  emp_id     bigint PRIMARY KEY,
  dept_id    integer,          -- nullable on purpose
  name       text NOT NULL,
  salary     numeric(12,2),
  hired_on   date,
  manager_id bigint
);

INSERT INTO dept (dept_id, dept_name) VALUES
  (1,'Engineering'), (2,'Sales'), (3,'Marketing'), (4,'Legal'), (5,'Empty Dept');

INSERT INTO emp (emp_id, dept_id, name, salary, hired_on, manager_id) VALUES
  (1, 1, 'Ada',   9000.00, '2021-03-01', NULL),
  (2, 1, 'Bob',   7000.00, '2021-07-15', 1),
  (3, 1, 'Cleo',  7000.00, '2022-01-10', 1),
  (4, 2, 'Dan',   5000.00, '2020-05-01', NULL),
  (5, 2, 'Eve',   4500.00, '2022-09-01', 4),
  (6, 2, 'Fay',   4500.00, '2023-02-01', 4),
  (7, 3, 'Gus',   6000.00, '2021-11-01', NULL),
  (8, 3, 'Hal',   5500.00, '2023-06-01', 7),
  (9, 4, 'Ivy',   8000.00, '2020-01-15', NULL),
  (10,1, 'Jan',   6500.00, '2023-08-01', 2),
  (11,NULL,'Kai',  3000.00, '2023-09-01', NULL),
  (12,1, 'Leo',   NULL,   '2024-01-15', 2);
```

Counts to memorise: `dept` = 5 rows, `emp` = 12 rows, employees with a non-null `dept_id` = 11, departments with at least one employee = 4.

| Join | Condition | Result | Row count | Why |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `CROSS JOIN dept, emp` | none | every dept paired with every emp | 5 × 12 = **60** | Cartesian product |
| `INNER JOIN ON d.dept_id = e.dept_id` | keys match | 11 employees joined to their dept; Kai dropped (NULL dept), Empty Dept dropped | **11** | NULL never matches, and no emp is in dept 5 |
| `LEFT JOIN FROM emp` | `d.dept_id = e.dept_id` | all 12 emps, Kai with `dept_name = NULL` | **12** | Left side preserved, right side padded |
| `RIGHT JOIN` (Postgres only) | mirror | all 5 depts, Empty Dept with `emp_id = NULL` | **5** (+ any unmatched left rows: none here) | Rewrite as `LEFT JOIN` from the other table; portable engines lack `RIGHT`/`FULL` (though SQLite and modern MySQL have them) |
| `FULL OUTER JOIN` | mirror | all 12 emps + Empty Dept | **13** | 11 matched + Kai + Empty Dept |
| `LEFT JOIN FROM dept` | `d.dept_id = e.dept_id` | all 5 depts, Empty Dept with NULL emp columns | **5** | Good pattern for "show all depts and any employees" |
| `SELF JOIN` `emp m JOIN emp e ON e.manager_id = m.emp_id` | manager matches emp | managers with reports: Ada(2), Dan(2), Gus(1), Bob(1) = 4 rows | **6** | Ada manages Bob and Cleo; Bob manages Jan and Leo; Dan manages Eve and Fay; Gus manages Hal; roots are excluded |
| `CROSS JOIN LATERAL ... LIMIT 1` | correlated, per outer row | one row per outer row | = outer row count | Top-1-per-group pattern |

Worked example with the full output:
```sql
SELECT d.dept_id, d.dept_name, e.emp_id, e.name
FROM   dept d
LEFT JOIN emp e ON e.dept_id = d.dept_id
ORDER  BY d.dept_id, e.emp_id;
```
```
 dept_id | dept_name  | emp_id | name
---------+------------+--------+------
       1 | Engineering|      1 | Ada
       1 | Engineering|      2 | Bob
       1 | Engineering|      3 | Cleo
       1 | Engineering|     10 | Jan
       1 | Engineering|     12 | Leo
       2 | Sales      |      4 | Dan
       2 | Sales      |      5 | Eve
       2 | Sales      |      6 | Fay
       3 | Marketing  |      7 | Gus
       3 | Marketing|      8 | Hal
       4 | Legal      |      9 | Ivy
       5 | Empty Dept |        |
```

**Questions**
1. Row count of `dept CROSS JOIN emp` on this fixture? (60.)
2. Why does an inner join drop Kai? (`NULL = 1` is UNKNOWN, not TRUE.)
3. `LEFT JOIN` from `emp` vs from `dept`: 12 rows vs 5 rows. Which side's count wins?
4. Why do you rarely see `RIGHT JOIN` in real code? (It is Postgres/SQLite/MySQL-specific syntax and forces the opposite mental direction; a `LEFT JOIN` with the tables swapped is portable and easier to read.)
5. `FULL OUTER JOIN` returns 13 rows here. Enumerate them.
6. Row count of the self join for managers? (6.)
7. What does a `LEFT JOIN` with no `ON` clause do? (It is allowed in Postgres and behaves like a cross join with all right columns NULL.)
8. When do you want `FULL OUTER JOIN`? (Reconciliation of two datasets; "what changed" diffs. Rare in application code.)
9. What breaks if you `INNER JOIN` a nullable FK in a reporting query?
10. Why is `CROSS JOIN` with a `WHERE` sometimes faster than an `INNER JOIN`? (Rarely; the optimizer transforms them. But `CROSS JOIN LATERAL` with `LIMIT` genuinely changes the plan shape, unlike a plain cross join.)

**Mistakes:** assuming an inner join preserves rows; forgetting `NULL` on a nullable FK drops those rows; using `RIGHT JOIN` in a codebase that has to run on more than one engine; chaining several `LEFT JOIN`s and losing track of which side can be NULL.

---

## 6.3.2 Join Explosion

Multiplication is the rule: if table A has `a` rows and B has `b` rows and you join on a non-unique key, you get up to `a × b` rows.

```sql
-- 12 employees x up to 2 managers each
SELECT e.emp_id, e.name, m.name AS manager
FROM   emp e
LEFT JOIN emp m ON m.emp_id = e.manager_id
ORDER  BY e.emp_id;
```
```
 emp_id | name | manager
--------+------+--------
      1 | Ada  |
      2 | Bob  | Ada
      3 | Cleo | Ada
      4 | Dan  |
      5 | Eve  | Dan
      6 | Fay  | Dan
      7 | Gus  |
      8 | Hal  | Gus
      9 | Ivy  |
     10 | Jan  | Bob
     11 | Kai  |
     12 | Leo  | Bob
```
12 rows here. Now join in a one-to-many child table: if the average employee has 20 order lines, the same query with a third join produces 12 × 20 = 240 rows, and any `count(*)`, `sum()`, or `avg()` in the `SELECT` list is now computing over the exploded set. `SELECT d.dept_name, count(*) FROM dept d JOIN emp e ... JOIN order_line ol ON ol.emp_id = e.emp_id` counts **order lines**, not employees, and engineers lose hours to this.

**Three ways to prevent it**
1. Aggregate the child first in a subquery or CTE, then join the pre-aggregated single row per parent.
2. Use a window function for a per-parent value: `sum(ol.amount) OVER (PARTITION BY e.emp_id)` — but note this also explodes; wrap it or use `DISTINCT` carefully (a `DISTINCT` on an exploded set is still correct for the partitioned aggregate because the value is constant per partition, but it is easy to get wrong with additional columns).
3. Use `EXISTS` / `IN` instead of a join when you only need to know whether a match exists. `EXISTS` short-circuits and never multiplies.

**The classic bug**
```sql
-- WRONG: counts order lines and labels the result "employees"
SELECT e.name, count(*) AS order_count
FROM   emp e JOIN order_line ol ON ol.emp_id = e.emp_id
GROUP  BY e.emp_id, e.name;

-- RIGHT: pre-aggregate
SELECT e.name, coalesce(l.order_count, 0) AS order_count
FROM   emp e
LEFT JOIN (
  SELECT emp_id, count(*) AS order_count FROM order_line GROUP BY emp_id
) l ON l.emp_id = e.emp_id;
```

**Another classic:** `SELECT * FROM a JOIN b ... JOIN c ...` where `a` has 2 rows matching each of 5 in `b` and each of 5 in `c`: 2 + 5 + 5 = 12 columns but 2 × 5 × 5 = 50 rows.

**Model answer, Q3:** "Context: a customer list page that showed 900 customers but the SQL said `count(*)` over 40,000, because each customer had 40 addresses and I joined `customer` to `address` for a primary-address flag. Decision: never join a one-to-many child just to filter or decorate; either pre-aggregate the child into one row per parent, or move the condition into a semi-join. Reasoning: a join between two relations produces the product of the matching tuples, so any aggregate over the joined set counts the child's multiplicity, not the parent's rows. The failure is silent because the number is plausible. Failure scenario: a `SUM(amount)` over an exploded join double-counts revenue by the number of joined child rows, and the discrepancy is exactly proportional to the fan-out, which makes it hard to spot. Prevention: a lint rule that rejects `count(*)` or `sum()` in a query with more than one join without an intermediate `GROUP BY` in a subquery, plus a check on expected row counts in tests."

**Questions**
1. Define join explosion and give the multiplication rule.
2. Why does `count(*)` after two one-to-many joins mislead?
3. `SUM(amount)` explodes: how do you fix it?
4. Why does `EXISTS` not multiply rows?
5. You join 3 tables and expect 100 rows and get 12,000. What are the likely causes?
6. A `LEFT JOIN` to a child table: how do you keep the parent row and still get a count?
7. When is `DISTINCT` after an explosion correct, and when is it a bug?
8. How do you detect explosion in `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` output?
9. Why is `count(DISTINCT parent_id)` a fragile fix?
10. Where in a query tree does the multiplication happen?

**Mistakes:** aggregating over an exploded join; `count(DISTINCT x)` as a universal fix (it hides the real cardinality and forces a sort/hash); adding `DISTINCT` to make numbers look right; joining a one-to-many table purely to test existence.

---

## 6.3.3 Semi-Joins and Anti-Joins

The three idioms for "rows in A matching (or not matching) B", and the NULL trap that separates them.

```sql
-- B-side data: notice NULLs in project_id
CREATE TABLE emp_project (emp_id int, project_id int, hours numeric(8,2));
INSERT INTO emp_project VALUES
  (1, 10, 40), (1, 11, 10),   -- Ada on two projects
  (2, 10, 20),                -- Bob on project 10
  (4, NULL, 5),               -- Dan on an unknown project
  (5, NULL, 5);               -- Eve on an unknown project
```

```sql
-- (1) SEMI-JOIN with EXISTS  -- returns 11 rows? No: 2 rows. See output.
SELECT e.emp_id, e.name
FROM   emp e
WHERE  EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM emp_project p WHERE p.emp_id = e.emp_id)
ORDER  BY e.emp_id;
```
```
 emp_id | name
--------+------
      1 | Ada
      2 | Bob
```
Two rows: Ada and Bob. `EXISTS` is a semi-join — it filters A without adding B's columns or duplicating A's rows. Ada appears **once**, even though she has two project rows. This is the correct default for "does this customer have orders?".

```sql
-- (2) ANTI-JOIN with NOT EXISTS  -- correct even when project_id has NULLs
SELECT e.emp_id, e.name
FROM   emp e
WHERE  NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM emp_project p WHERE p.emp_id = e.emp_id)
ORDER  BY e.emp_id;
```
```
 emp_id | name
--------+------
      3 | Cleo
      4 | Dan
      5 | Eve
      6 | Fay
      7 | Gus
      8 | Hal
      9 | Ivy
     10 | Jan
     11 | Kai
     12 | Leo
```
Ten rows: everyone except Ada and Bob. Dan and Eve *are* in `emp_project` (with a NULL project), and they correctly do **not** appear in the anti-join, because the correlation predicate is `p.emp_id = e.emp_id`, which is TRUE for them.

```sql
-- (3) ANTI-JOIN with LEFT JOIN ... IS NULL
SELECT e.emp_id, e.name
FROM   emp e
LEFT JOIN emp_project p ON p.emp_id = e.emp_id
WHERE  p.emp_id IS NULL
ORDER  BY e.emp_id;
```
```
 emp_id | name
--------+------
      3 | Cleo
      4 | Dan
      5 | Eve
      6 | Fay
      7 | Gus
      8 | Hal
      9 | Ivy
     10 | Jan
     11 | Kai
     12 | Leo
```
Identical ten rows. The `LEFT JOIN` produced 4 rows (Ada twice, Bob once, Dan, Eve) and the `IS NULL` filter removed the 4 matched ones. Key caveat: the `IS NULL` test must be on a column that is `NOT NULL` in B (the join key), because a matched row can also have NULL in other B columns.

```sql
-- (4) NOT IN -- THE TRAP
SELECT e.emp_id, e.name
FROM   emp e
WHERE  e.emp_id NOT IN (SELECT p.emp_id FROM emp_project p)
ORDER  BY e.emp_id;
```
```
 emp_id | name
--------+------
(zero rows)
```
`emp_project` contains a NULL `emp_id`? No — but suppose the subquery is `(SELECT p.project_id FROM emp_project p)`:
```sql
SELECT e.emp_id, e.name
FROM   emp e
```
