```sql
WHERE  e.emp_id NOT IN (SELECT p.project_id FROM emp_project p)
ORDER  BY e.emp_id;
```
```
 emp_id | name
--------+------
(zero rows)
```
Because the subquery result contains NULL, `emp_id NOT IN (10, 11, 10, NULL, NULL)` expands to `emp_id <> 10 AND emp_id <> 11 AND emp_id <> NULL`, and `emp_id <> NULL` is UNKNOWN for every row, so the whole predicate is never TRUE. **A single NULL in a `NOT IN` list makes the query return nothing.**

**Rule: never use `NOT IN` with a subquery you do not control. Use `NOT EXISTS`, or filter the NULLs out (`WHERE x IS NOT NULL`).** `NOT IN` with a *literal* list is safe (`WHERE status NOT IN ('a','b')`).

| Idiom | Correct with NULLs in B? | Duplicates A? | Plan | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `EXISTS` | Yes | No | Semi-join (hash semi join) | Default choice |
| `NOT EXISTS` | Yes | No | Anti-join (hash anti join) | Default anti-join |
| `LEFT JOIN ... IS NULL` | Yes, if you test a `NOT NULL` join column | No | Nested loop anti join or hash anti join | Also good for fetching columns from a *different* related table in the same pass |
| `NOT IN (subquery)` | **No** | No | Often a hashed subplan; optimizer may transform it, but the NULL semantics still apply | Avoid |
| `LEFT JOIN ... WHERE b.col IS NULL` on a nullable B column | **No** | No | Silently wrong | Avoid; test the join key instead |

**Questions**
1. What is a semi-join, and what does it return?
2. Why does `EXISTS` not duplicate rows for an employee with two projects?
3. Write an anti-join three ways. Which is safest, and why?
4. Construct data that makes `NOT IN` return zero rows. What is the mechanism?
5. `LEFT JOIN ... WHERE b.some_nullable_col IS NULL` — what does that actually compute?
6. When is `LEFT JOIN ... IS NULL` better than `NOT EXISTS`? (When you also need to read a column from a *different* table in the same pass, e.g. "the latest order's status".)
7. How do you find the top-3 rows per group without a window function? (`LEFT JOIN` + `count`/`array_agg`, or three self-joins.)
8. Why does `NOT IN` with a literal list behave fine?
9. What plan does an anti-join produce, and why is that efficient?
10. Rewrite `WHERE id NOT IN (SELECT ...)` to be NULL-safe in one line.

**Model answer, Q4:** "Context: a nightly job that emails users who have not logged in for 90 days. The query was `WHERE u.last_login_at NOT IN (SELECT last_login_at FROM logins WHERE ...)`. It returned zero rows and nobody noticed for a week. Decision: replace every `NOT IN` subquery with `NOT EXISTS`, and add a lint rule. Reasoning: `NOT IN` is three-valued. The subquery result contains a NULL because some rows have no login, so the predicate becomes `x <> v1 AND x <> v2 AND ... AND x <> NULL`, and the last term is UNKNOWN for every row, so the entire `AND` can never be TRUE. `NOT EXISTS` evaluates the correlated predicate row by row and yields TRUE or FALSE with no third state. Trade-off: `NOT IN` with a hashed subplan can be marginally cheaper for small, NULL-free lists, which is not a reason to risk it. Failure scenario: a campaign targeting a segment silently sends to nobody, and the metric is reported as "no users qualified", which looks like a data problem rather than a logic bug. Prevention: never write `NOT IN (subquery)`; if you must, add `WHERE x IS NOT NULL` inside the subquery."

**Mistakes:** `NOT IN` with NULLs; testing a nullable B column for `IS NULL` after a left join; believing `EXISTS` duplicates A's rows; using a `LEFT JOIN` where `EXISTS` is clearer.

---

## 6.3.4 LATERAL Joins, Join Order, and the `WHERE`-vs-`ON` Bug

**`LATERAL`**: a subquery in `FROM` that can reference columns of preceding `FROM` items, evaluated once per outer row. Postgres 9.3+, MySQL 8.0.14+, SQL Server 2005+, SQLite 3.35+ (with `LATERAL` keyword support from 3.35; SQLite instead supports the correlated-table-in-`FROM` idiom via `CROSS JOIN`).

```sql
-- Top 2 highest-paid employees per department
SELECT d.dept_name, e.name, e.salary
FROM   dept d
CROSS JOIN LATERAL (
  SELECT name, salary
  FROM   emp
  WHERE  dept_id = d.dept_id
  ORDER  BY salary DESC NULLS LAST, emp_id
  LIMIT  2
) e
ORDER  BY d.dept_id, e.salary DESC;
```
```
 dept_name  | name | salary
------------+------+--------
 Engineering| Ada  | 9000.00
 Engineering| Bob  | 7000.00
 Engineering| Cleo | 7000.00
 Sales      | Dan  | 5000.00
 Sales      | Eve  | 4500.00
 Sales      | Fay  | 4500.00
 Marketing  | Gus  | 6000.00
 Marketing  | Hal  | 5500.00
 Legal      | Ivy  | 8000.00
 Empty Dept |      |
```
Ten rows. Note: Engineering gets **three** rows because Bob and Cleo tie at 7000 and `LIMIT 2` is a row limit, not a rank limit. This is exactly the tie semantics interviewers probe: `LIMIT 2` = top two *rows*; `RANK() <= 2` = top two *ranks* (all tied rows included). `Empty Dept` gets one row with NULLs because `LATERAL` always returns exactly one row per outer row for a scalar-returning `LIMIT` query — the "row" is all NULLs when there are no matches. Use `INNER JOIN LATERAL` to drop those.

**LATERAL is the right tool when**
- you need top-N-per-group *without* a window function (and it is often the fastest form, because each group gets an index scan with `LIMIT`),
- you need an aggregate per outer row where the aggregate is over a *correlated* set,
- you need a correlated subquery in `FROM` for an optimizer-friendly plan (the anti-join form).

```sql
-- "Most recent order per customer" as a lateral, with the status column available
SELECT c.customer_id, o.ordered_at, o.status
FROM   customer c
LEFT JOIN LATERAL (
  SELECT o.ordered_at, o.status
  FROM   orders o
  WHERE  o.customer_id = c.customer_id
  ORDER  BY o.ordered_at DESC
  LIMIT  1
) o ON true;
```
`LEFT JOIN LATERAL ... ON true` is the idiomatic "optional" form; it keeps customers with no orders and fills NULLs.

**Join order.** SQL join order is written left to right but executed by the optimizer's chosen order, which normally:
1. picks the driving relation (smallest estimated result after filters, from statistics),
2. joins the next relation using the cheapest access path (index nested loop, hash join, merge join),
3. postpones expensive filters until after the join that reduces rows.

What you control: predicate placement (a highly selective `ON` condition lets the optimizer choose a nested loop early), materialization vs inlining of CTEs, and forcing with `join_collapse_limit`/`from_collapse_limit` (Postgres) or `STRAIGHT_JOIN`/`JOIN_ORDER` hints (MySQL). Joins to the *inner* side of an outer join cannot be reordered freely — the semantics pin them.

Practical heuristics: filter as early as possible; put the most selective predicate in the `ON` of the join that constrains it, not in a `WHERE` that runs after an outer join (see below); avoid joining more than 3-4 tables before the optimizer gives up on exhaustive planning (then it falls back to heuristics and can pick a bad order — `from_collapse_limit` exists precisely because of this).

**The `WHERE`-vs-`ON` outer-join bug**

```sql
-- INTENT: all employees, with their department name, showing NULL where there is no dept
SELECT e.name, d.dept_name
FROM   emp e
LEFT JOIN dept d ON e.dept_id = d.dept_id
WHERE  d.dept_name = 'Engineering';
-- Kai and every non-engineer disappear.
-- The LEFT JOIN produced NULLs for Kai, then WHERE filtered them out.
-- The result is an INNER JOIN with extra steps.
```

```sql
-- CORRECT: keep the LEFT JOIN semantics and filter afterwards, or filter before joining
SELECT e.name, d.dept_name
FROM   emp e
LEFT JOIN dept d ON e.dept_id = d.dept_id
WHERE  d.dept_name = 'Engineering';
-- still wrong on purpose: this is what people actually write. Use:
SELECT e.name, d.dept_name
FROM   emp e
JOIN   dept d ON d.dept_name = 'Engineering'   -- constant -> can be a filter
WHERE  e.dept_id = d.dept_id;                  -- equivalent to INNER JOIN
```

The rule: **a predicate on the null-supplying side of an outer join belongs in the `ON` clause if you want to preserve left rows.**

```sql
-- Correct way to say "all employees, but only Engineering ones shown":
SELECT e.name, d.dept_name
FROM   emp e
LEFT JOIN dept d
       ON d.dept_id = e.dept_id
      AND d.dept_name = 'Engineering'
WHERE  d.dept_name = 'Engineering'   -- this would filter again; so instead use an EXISTS-style semi-join:
```

The genuinely correct formulation for "all employees, Engineering dept info when applicable" is:
```sql
SELECT e.name, d.dept_name
FROM   emp e
LEFT JOIN dept d
       ON d.dept_id = e.dept_id
      AND d.dept_name = 'Engineering';
```
```
 name  | dept_name
-------+------------
 Ada   | Engineering
 Bob   | Engineering
 Cleo  | Engineering
 Dan   |
 Eve   |
 Fay   |
 Gus   |
 Hal   |
 Ivy   |
 Jan   | Engineering
 Kai   |
 Leo   | Engineering
```
Twelve rows: Engineering employees get the name, everyone else gets NULL. This is the only correct shape for "optional filtered lookup".

Also note the other half of the rule: `WHERE` on the **preserved** side (the left table of a `LEFT JOIN`) does *not* break the outer join; it just filters the preserved side. And for an `INNER JOIN` the distinction is meaningless.

**Questions**
1. What does `LATERAL` allow, and what does it evaluate per outer row?
2. Top-2 per department with `LATERAL`: how many rows for Engineering, and why?
3. `LIMIT 2` vs `RANK() <= 2` with ties — what differs?
4. `LEFT JOIN LATERAL ... ON true` vs `INNER JOIN LATERAL`: which rows differ?
5. When is `LATERAL` faster than a window function for top-N-per-group? (When each group has its own selective index and the limit is small; a nested loop with `LIMIT` stops early, while a window function must rank the whole partition.)
6. Does the optimizer respect my join order? (No, it reorders unless you disable reordering or hint.)
7. Explain the `WHERE`-vs-`ON` outer join bug and give the corrected query.
8. Where do you put a predicate that filters only the right table of a `LEFT JOIN`?
9. What is `from_collapse_limit` for?
10. When is a CTE materialized and forced to be materialized, and why does that change join order?

**Mistakes:** putting right-side filters in `WHERE` after a `LEFT JOIN`; `LIMIT N` presented as "top N ranks"; assuming written join order is executed order; using `LATERAL` where a window function would let the optimizer share work; using `LATERAL` inside a loop where a single window function is one pass.

---

# 6.4 Subqueries, CTEs, and Window Functions

Intermediate to Advanced · **Must-know** (window functions are the single highest-yield SQL topic at senior level)

## 6.4.1 Subquery Shapes and Decorrelation

| Shape | Syntax | Returns | Correlated? | Use when |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Scalar | `WHERE salary > (SELECT avg(salary) FROM emp)` | Exactly one row/one column, or NULL | Often | Comparing to a single computed value |
| Row/row constructor | `WHERE (a, b) IN ((1,2),(3,4))` | Row-wise membership | No | Small IN-lists of tuples (Postgres 9.5+) |
| IN | `WHERE dept_id IN (SELECT dept_id FROM emp WHERE salary > 6000)` | A set | No | Membership, with the NULL caveat |
| EXISTS / NOT EXISTS | `WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 ... WHERE correlated)` | Boolean | Yes | Semi/anti join; NULL-safe; usually the best plan |
| Derived table | `FROM (SELECT ...) t` | A relation | No | Reusable sub-results, aggregation before join |
| LATERAL derived | `FROM a, LATERAL (SELECT ... WHERE x = a.y LIMIT n) s` | A relation per outer row | Yes | Top-N-per-group, correlated aggregates |
| `ARRAY(...)` | `= ANY (ARRAY[...])`, `= ANY (SELECT arr FROM ...)` | A set | No | Array membership and PostgreSQL-specific idiom `col = ANY(array_of_ids)` |

```sql
-- scalar
SELECT name, salary FROM emp
WHERE salary > (SELECT avg(salary) FROM emp WHERE salary IS NOT NULL)
ORDER BY salary DESC;
```
`(SELECT avg(salary) FROM emp)` = `30,500.00 / 11` = `2772.727...`. Employees above that: all 11 with a salary (lowest is Kai at 3000). Leo has NULL salary so `salary > 2772.72` is UNKNOWN and Leo is excluded — a good place to note the behaviour.

```sql
-- IN with a NULL trap, guarded
SELECT name FROM dept
WHERE dept_id IN (SELECT dept_id FROM emp WHERE salary > 8000);
-- dept_id 1 (Ada is 9000)
SELECT name FROM dept
WHERE dept_id NOT IN (SELECT dept_id FROM emp);
-- zero rows: emp contains dept_id NULL (Kai)

-- EXISTS: NULL-safe, no row multiplication, usually a hash semi join
SELECT d.dept_name
FROM   dept d
WHERE  EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM emp e WHERE e.dept_id = d.dept_id AND e.salary IS NOT NULL)
ORDER  BY d.dept_name;
```
Every department except Empty Dept: Engineering, Legal, Marketing, Sales.

**Correlated subqueries and decorrelation.** A correlated subquery is re-executed per outer row, or converted by the optimizer into a semi/anti/scalar join. Postgres decorrelates many forms (`IN` → hash semi join, `EXISTS` → semi join, `= (SELECT ...)` → nested loop), but not always, and not for `LIMIT` or aggregates inside. The practical rules:

```sql
-- (a) correlated EXISTS: ideal, decorrelates to a hash semi join
SELECT c.customer_id, c.name
FROM   customer c
WHERE  EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = c.customer_id);

-- (b) correlated scalar subquery in SELECT: decorrelates to a nested loop or hash join
SELECT d.dept_name,
       (SELECT count(*) FROM emp e WHERE e.dept_id = d.dept_id) AS headcount
FROM   dept d;
-- Empty Dept -> 0, not NULL, because count() over zero rows is 0.
-- If you use max(salary) instead, Empty Dept -> NULL, which is different and often a bug.

-- (c) correlated subquery with LIMIT: CANNOT be decorrelated; it runs per outer row
SELECT c.customer_id
FROM   customer c
WHERE  (SELECT o.ordered_at FROM orders o
        WHERE o.customer_id = c.customer_id
        ORDER BY o.ordered_at DESC LIMIT 1) > '2024-06-01';
-- Rewrite as LATERAL (same cost, clearer) or a DISTINCT ON / window function
-- (which scans once):
SELECT customer_id FROM (
  SELECT DISTINCT ON (customer_id) customer_id, ordered_at
  FROM   orders ORDER BY customer_id, ordered_at DESC
) t WHERE ordered_at > '2024-06-01';

-- (d) correlated aggregate in WHERE: usually decorrelated
SELECT name FROM emp e
WHERE salary > (SELECT avg(salary) FROM emp e2 WHERE e2.dept_id = e.dept_id);
```

The interview-relevant point: **`SELECT (agg) FROM t WHERE corr` can become `LEFT JOIN (SELECT corr, agg FROM t GROUP BY corr) ON ...`, and Postgres will usually do that for you, but it will not do it for `LIMIT` or for a `DISTINCT`-producing subquery.** Knowing which shapes decorrelate is the difference between "I wrote SQL" and "I understand the planner".

**NULL in a scalar subquery**
```sql
SELECT (SELECT max(salary) FROM emp WHERE dept_id = 999) AS x;   -- NULL
-- and: WHERE (SELECT ...) IS NULL works, but WHERE (SELECT ...) = NULL never works
```

## 6.4.2 CTEs and Recursive CTEs

A CTE (`WITH`) is a named subquery. It does not create a temporary table by default; it is inlined into the main query (Postgres 12+), which means it can be pulled up, reordered, and joined like any other relation.

```sql
WITH active AS (
  SELECT * FROM emp WHERE status = 'active'
), dept_totals AS (
  SELECT dept_id, sum(salary) AS payroll FROM active GROUP BY dept_id
)
SELECT * FROM dept_totals WHERE payroll > 10000;
```

**Materialization behaviour, precisely**

| Situation | Postgres behaviour | Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| Referenced exactly once, side-effect-free, non-recursive | Inlined (pre-12: always materialized) | The planner may reorder, push down predicates, and choose any join order |
| Referenced more than once | Materialized by default (pre-12 too) | Predicates from the outer query cannot be pushed in, so stats come from the CTE's own estimate and the join order is constrained |
| `WITH MATERIALIZED` | Always materialized | Forces a CTE Scan, forbids predicate pushdown; useful as an optimization fence or to compute once for reuse |
| Recursive (`WITH RECURSIVE`) | Materialized working table, iterated | The recursive term runs to completion; you cannot push predicates into the recursive reference |
| Contains a volatile function or `LIMIT` | Generally materialized | The function is evaluated once; side effects are not duplicated |

This is why the classic bug happens:
```sql
WITH heavy AS MATERIALIZED (
  SELECT ... FROM huge_table WHERE complex_predicate
)
SELECT * FROM heavy WHERE id = $1;
```
The `WHERE id = $1` cannot be pushed into the CTE, so Postgres computes the whole `huge_table` scan and then filters. Removing `MATERIALIZED` (or, in older versions, inlining the subquery manually) restores the index scan. This is a *performance* difference, not a semantic one — but it is the difference between 2ms and 4 minutes.

**Recursive CTEs — five real shapes.**

*1. Org chart with depth and materialized path*
```sql
CREATE TABLE emp (
  emp_id bigint PRIMARY KEY,
  manager_id bigint REFERENCES emp(emp_id),
  name text NOT NULL
);
-- Ada(1) -> Bob(2) -> Jan(10), Leo(12); Ada -> Cleo(3); Dan(4) -> Eve(5), Fay(6);
-- Gus(7) -> Hal(8); Ivy(9) is a root.

WITH RECURSIVE org AS (
  -- anchor: roots
  SELECT e.emp_id, e.manager_id, e.name, 0 AS depth,
         ARRAY[e.emp_id]::bigint[] AS path
  FROM   emp e
  WHERE  e.manager_id IS NULL
  UNION ALL
  -- recursive term: direct reports of anything already in `org`
  SELECT e.emp_id, e.manager_id, e.name, o.depth + 1,
         o.path || e.emp_id
  FROM   emp e
  JOIN   org o ON e.manager_id = o.emp_id
  WHERE  NOT e.emp_id = ANY(o.path)      -- cycle guard
)
SELECT repeat('  ', depth) || name AS tree,
       depth,
       array_to_string(path, ' > ') AS path
FROM   org
ORDER BY path;
```
```
 tree              | depth | path
-------------------+-------+---------------------
 Ada               |     0 | 1
   Cleo            |     1 | 1 > 3
   Bob             |     1 | 1 > 2
     Jan           |     2 | 1 > 2 > 10
     Leo           |     2 | 1 > 2 > 12
 Dan               |     0 | 4
   Eve             |     1 | 4 > 5
   Fay             |     1 | 4 > 6
 Gus               |     0 | 7
   Hal             |     1 | 7 > 8
 Ivy               |     0 | 9
```
`ORDER BY path` works because `bigint[]` comparison is element-wise lexicographic, which coincides with depth-first order for a tree with ascending ids. Note the cycle guard: `WHERE NOT e.emp_id = ANY(o.path)` is what stops an accidental cycle (`a → b → a`) from looping forever. Without it the CTE recurses until it exhausts memory.

*2. Category tree (adjacency list) flattened with a materialized path*
```sql
CREATE TABLE category (
  node_id   integer PRIMARY KEY,
  parent_id integer REFERENCES category(node_id),
  label     text NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO category VALUES
  (1,NULL,'Electronics'), (2,1,'Phones'), (3,2,'Smartphones'),
  (4,1,'Laptops'), (5,NULL,'Garden'), (6,5,'Tools');

WITH RECURSIVE cat AS (
  SELECT node_id, parent_id, label, 0 AS depth,
         ARRAY[node_id]::integer[] AS path
  FROM   category WHERE parent_id IS NULL
  UNION ALL
  SELECT c.node_id, c.parent_id, c.label, t.depth + 1, t.path || c.node_id
  FROM   category c JOIN cat t ON c.parent_id = t.node_id
)
SELECT repeat('  ', depth) || label AS tree,
       path,
       depth,
       (SELECT count(*) FROM category d
        WHERE d.node_id = ANY(path) AND d.node_id <> path[1]) AS descendants
FROM   cat ORDER BY path;
```
```
 tree                 | path    | depth | descendants
----------------------+---------+-------+-------------
 Electronics          | {1}     |     0 |          5
   Laptops            | {1,4}   |     1 |          0
   Phones             | {1,2}   |     1 |          2
     Smartphones      | {1,2,3} |     2 |          0
 Garden               | {5}     |     0 |          2
   Tools              | {5,6}   |     1 |          0
```
`descendants` uses `<@` containment on the path array: `(path @> ARRAY[d.node_id]) AND d.node_id <> path[1]`. This is the shape used by the `ltree` extension in production when you want index support on the hierarchy.

*3. Date series (calendar spine)*
```sql
WITH RECURSIVE days(day) AS (
  SELECT DATE '2024-01-01'
  UNION ALL
  SELECT day + 1 FROM days WHERE day < DATE '2024-01-05'
)
SELECT day FROM days ORDER BY day;
```
```
 day
------------
 2024-01-01
 2024-01-02
 2024-01-03
 2024-01-04
 2024-01-05
```
The join-to-spine pattern fills missing dates with zero so a time series has no gaps:
```sql
WITH RECURSIVE days(day) AS (
  SELECT DATE '2024-01-01'
  UNION ALL
  SELECT day + 1 FROM days WHERE day < DATE '2024-01-31'
)
SELECT d.day, coalesce(sum(o.amount), 0) AS revenue
FROM   days d
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.ordered_on = d.day AND o.status = 'paid'
GROUP  BY d.day ORDER BY d.day;
```
Notice the status filter is in the `ON` clause: put it in `WHERE` and the `LEFT JOIN` degenerates to an inner join and the zero-revenue days disappear.

*4. Path building with cycle detection and an explicit flag*
```sql
WITH RECURSIVE cat AS (
  SELECT node_id, parent_id, ARRAY[node_id]::integer[] AS path, false AS cyclic
  FROM   category WHERE parent_id IS NULL
  UNION ALL
  SELECT c.node_id, c.parent_id, t.path || c.node_id,
         c.node_id = ANY(t.path)
  FROM   category c JOIN cat t ON c.parent_id = t.node_id
  WHERE  NOT c.node_id = ANY(t.path)         -- don't even recurse into a cycle
)
SELECT node_id, array_to_string(path,'/') AS path, cyclic,
       cardinality(path) - 1 AS depth
FROM   cat ORDER BY path;
```

*5. Recursive CTE for a topological reduction (delete-orphans loop)*
```sql
-- repeatedly remove orders whose customer no longer exists; stops when no rows change
WITH RECURSIVE purge AS (
  SELECT order_id FROM orders o
  WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM customer c WHERE c.customer_id = o.customer_id)
  UNION ALL
  SELECT o.order_id FROM orders o
  JOIN purge p ON p.order_id = o.replaced_by   -- also cascade to replacements
)
DELETE FROM orders WHERE order_id IN (SELECT order_id FROM purge);
```

Notes on recursive CTE limits: Postgres 14+ allows multiple recursive references and mutual recursion (`WITH RECURSIVE a AS (...), b AS (...)` where `b` references `a` and `a`'s recursive term references `b`); it still runs serially, not in parallel; the working table is a `Tuplestore` so it materializes everything. MySQL 8.0 supports recursion with `cte_max_recursion_depth` (default 1000) and **requires `cte_recursive_depth`/`max_recursion_depth` to be raised** for deep hierarchies — a classic "it works in psql, fails in production" difference.

## 6.4.3 Window Functions: Syntax, Frames, and the Determinism Trap

A window function computes over a *window* of rows defined by `PARTITION BY` (the set), `ORDER BY` (the ordering inside the set), and the frame (which rows the aggregate sees). Crucially, a window function can appear in `SELECT` and `ORDER BY` **and cannot appear in `WHERE`**, and — unlike a `GROUP BY` — it does **not** collapse rows.

**Determinism first.** Every window function with an `ORDER BY` needs a unique tie-breaker to be reproducible. With ties, which row gets `row_number() = 1` is not defined, and two runs can return different rows.

Fixture used throughout this section:
```sql
CREATE TABLE sales (
  sale_id integer PRIMARY KEY,
  region  text NOT NULL,
  product text NOT NULL,
  amount  numeric(10,2) NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO sales VALUES
  (1,'East','Widget',100.00),
  (2,'East','Widget',100.00),
  (3,'East','Gadget',200.00),
  (4,'West','Widget',300.00),
  (5,'West','Gadget',150.00),
  (6,'West','Widget',300.00);
```

**`ROW_NUMBER` vs `RANK` vs `DENSE_RANK` vs `NTILE`, exact output**
```sql
SELECT sale_id, region, amount,
       row_number() OVER (PARTITION BY region ORDER BY amount DESC, sale_id) AS rn,
       rank()       OVER (PARTITION BY region ORDER BY amount DESC, sale_id) AS rnk,
       dense_rank() OVER (PARTITION BY region ORDER BY amount DESC, sale_id) AS drnk,
       ntile(2)     OVER (ORDER BY amount DESC, sale_id)                  AS half
FROM   sales
ORDER  BY region, rn;
```
```
 sale_id | region | amount  | rn | rnk | drnk | half
---------+--------+---------+----+-----+------+------
       3 | East   |  200.00 |  1 |   1 |    1 |    1
       1 | East   |  100.00 |  2 |   2 |    2 |    2
       2 | East   |  100.00 |  3 |   2 |    2 |    2
       4 | West   |  300.00 |  1 |   1 |    1 |    1
       6 | West   |  300.00 |  2 |   1 |    1 |    1
       5 | West   |  150.00 |  3 |   3 |    2 |    2
```
Read the tie behaviour: East has two rows at 100.00 and they take ranks 2,2 (West has two rows at 300.00 and they take ranks 1,1). `RANK` leaves gaps (West's 150.00 is rank **3**, not 2); `DENSE_RANK` does not (it is 2). `ROW_NUMBER` always produces 1..n with no gaps and breaks ties arbitrarily, hence `sale_id` in the `ORDER BY`. `NTILE(2)` splits the whole 6-row set into two buckets of 3 by amount, so it assigns 1 to the top 3 amounts (300, 300, 200) and 2 to the rest (150, 100, 100) — note the bucket assignment is *not* the same as "top half by rank" once ties exist.

| Function | Ties | Gaps after a tie | Use for |
|---|---|---|---|
| `ROW_NUMBER` | Arbitrary (needs a tie-breaker) | No | "Exactly N rows", paging, "pick one canonical row" |
| `RANK` | Shared | Yes | "Top 2 ranks" competition semantics, e.g. Olympic ranking |
| `DENSE_RANK` | Shared | No | "Distinct value ranks", bucketing into dense tiers |
| `NTILE(n)` | n-1 boundaries | n/a | Pagination buckets, quintiles, cohort splits |
| `PERCENT_RANK` | Shared | Yes | `(rank-1)/(rows-1)`, 0.0 to 1.0 |
| `CUME_DIST` | Shared | Yes | Fraction of rows with rank <= current, useful for `WHERE cume_dist <= 0.1` |

```sql
SELECT sale_id, region, amount,
       round(percent_rank() OVER (PARTITION BY region ORDER BY amount DESC, sale_id)::numeric, 4) AS pct_rank,
       round(cume_dist()    OVER (PARTITION BY region ORDER BY amount DESC, sale_id)::numeric, 4) AS cume_dist
FROM   sales ORDER BY region, sale_id;
```
```
 sale_id | region | amount  | pct_rank | cume_dist
---------+--------+---------+----------+-----------
       1 | East   |  100.00 |    0.5000 |    1.0000
       2 | East   |  100.00 |    0.5000 |    1.0000
       3 | East   |  200.00 |    0.0000 |    0.3333
       4 | West   |  300.00 |    0.0000 |    0.6667
       5 | West   |  150.00 |    1.0000 |    1.0000
       6 | West   |  300.00 |    0.0000 |    0.6667
```
`percent_rank = (rank - 1) / (partition_rows - 1)`; for West's 150.00 that is `(3-1)/(3-1) = 1.0`. `cume_dist` is the fraction of the partition at or below the current rank, so East's 200.00 is 1/3 and West's 300.00 is 2/3.

**Frames.** The frame decides which rows an aggregate window function sees.

| Frame | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `RANGE BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW` | **The default when `ORDER BY` is present.** All rows from the partition start through the last *peer* of the current row |
| `ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW` | Same but stops at the physical current row, so ties get different running totals |
| `ROWS BETWEEN n PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW` | Trailing window of n+1 physical rows (moving average) |
| `ROWS BETWEEN CURRENT ROW AND UNBOUNDED FOLLOWING` | Forward-looking window |
| `RANGE BETWEEN INTERVAL '7 days' PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW` | Time-based window (needs a date/timestamp `ORDER BY`) |
| `ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND UNBOUNDED FOLLOWING` | Whole partition |
| *no `ORDER BY`* | Whole partition as the frame; only `ORDER BY` is undefined |

**Running total, both ways**
```sql
-- Default frame (RANGE ... CURRENT ROW): peers share the same running total
SELECT sale_id, region, amount,
       sum(amount) OVER (PARTITION BY region ORDER BY amount) AS running_r
FROM sales ORDER BY region, amount;
```
```
 sale_id | region | amount  | running_r
---------+--------+---------+-----------
       3 | East   |  200.00 |   200.00
       1 | East   |  100.00 |   400.00
       2 | East   |  100.00 |   400.00
       4 | West   |  300.00 |   600.00
       6 | West   |  300.00 |   600.00
       5 | West   |  150.00 |   750.00
```
```
-- Explicit ROWS frame: physical-row order, ties get different totals
SELECT sale_id, region, amount,
       sum(amount) OVER (PARTITION BY region ORDER BY amount, sale_id
                         ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW) AS running_r
FROM sales ORDER BY region, amount, sale_id;
```
```
 sale_id | region | amount  | running_r
---------+--------+---------+-----------
       3 | East   |  200.00 |   200.00
       1 | East   |  100.00 |   300.00
       2 | East   |  100.00 |   400.00
       4 | West   |  300.00 |   300.00
       6 | West   |  300.00 |   600.00
       5 | West   |  150.00 |   750.00
```
This RANGE-vs-ROWS difference is the most common window-function bug and a favourite interview question.

**Moving average**
```sql
SELECT sale_id, amount,
       round(avg(amount) OVER (ORDER BY sale_id
                ROWS BETWEEN 2 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW)::numeric, 2) AS ma3
FROM   sales ORDER BY sale_id;
```
```
 sale_id | amount  | ma3
---------+---------+------
       1 |  100.00 | 100.00
       2 |  100.00 | 100.00
       3 |  200.00 | 133.33
       4 |  300.00 | 200.00
       5 |  150.00 | 216.67
       6 |  300.00 | 250.00
```
The first two rows average over fewer than three rows, which is correct behaviour and worth stating rather than hiding with `COALESCE`.

**`LAG` / `LEAD`**
```sql
SELECT sale_id, amount,
       lag(amount)  OVER (ORDER BY sale_id) AS prev,
       lead(amount) OVER (ORDER BY sale_id) AS nxt,
       amount - lag(amount) OVER (ORDER BY sale_id) AS delta
FROM   sales ORDER BY sale_id;
```
```
 sale_id | amount  | prev   | nxt   | delta
---------+---------+--------+-------+-------
       1 |  100.00 |        | 100.00|        <- NULL, not 0
       2 |  100.00 | 100.00 | 200.00|    0.00
       3 |  200.00 | 100.00 | 300.00|  100.00
       4 |  300.00 | 200.00 | 150.00|  100.00
       5 |  150.00 | 300.00 | 300.00| -150.00
       6 |  300.00 | 150.00 |       |  150.00
```
`lag(amount, 1, 0)` supplies a default instead of NULL. Always make the first row's NULL explicit when computing deltas, growth, or conversion rates — dividing by a NULL lag yields NULL, not an error, which quietly drops the first period from a chart.

**The `FIRST_VALUE` / `LAST_VALUE` frame pitfall**
```sql
-- LAST_VALUE with the DEFAULT frame returns the CURRENT ROW's value, not the partition's last
SELECT sale_id, region, amount,
       last_value(amount) OVER (PARTITION BY region ORDER BY amount, sale_id) AS lv_wrong,
       last_value(amount) OVER (PARTITION BY region ORDER BY amount, sale_id
              ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND UNBOUNDED FOLLOWING)   AS lv_right
FROM   sales ORDER BY region, sale_id;
```
```
 sale_id | region | amount  | lv_wrong | lv_right
---------+--------+---------+----------+---------
       1 | East   |  100.00 |   100.00 |   200.00
       2 | East   |  100.00 |   100.00 |   200.00
       3 | East   |  200.00 |   200.00 |   200.00
       4 | West   |  300.00 |   300.00 |   150.00
       5 | West   |  150.00 |   150.00 |   150.00
       6 | West   |  300.00 |   300.00 |   150.00
```
Because the default frame ends at the current row, `last_value` is a copy of the current row. `first_value` is *not* affected by the default frame (the frame always starts at `UNBOUNDED PRECEDING`), which is exactly why the pitfall surprises people who learned `first_value` first. The same reasoning applies to `nth_value`. Fix: always write the frame explicitly when using `last_value`, `nth_value`, or any aggregate you intend as a "whole group" value.

The related ordering trap: a window function's `ORDER BY` controls *window ordering*, not output order. Always add a separate outer `ORDER BY` for the result order.

**Named windows**
```sql
-- a named window is a reusable frame specification
SELECT sale_id,
       sum(amount) OVER w AS running_total,
       avg(amount) OVER w AS running_avg
FROM   sales
WINDOW w AS (ORDER BY sale_id ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW)
ORDER  BY sale_id;
```
Named windows keep frame definitions in one place and can be copied into another query verbatim. Postgres also allows `WINDOW w AS (...), w2 AS (w ORDER BY region)` for incremental modification.

**`FILTER` on aggregates**
```sql
SELECT count(*)                                   AS all_rows,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE region = 'East')   AS east_rows,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE amount > 150)      AS big_rows,
       sum(amount) FILTER (WHERE product = 'Widget') AS widget_total
FROM   sales;
```
```
 all_rows | east_rows | big_rows | widget_total
----------+-----------+----------+--------------
        6 |         3 |        4 |       700.00
```
Widget amounts: 100 + 100 + 300 + 300 = 700.00. `FILTER` is Postgres (and standard SQL:2011); the portable spelling is `sum(CASE WHEN product = 'Widget' THEN amount ELSE 0 END)` — but note that `ELSE 0` is wrong if amounts can be negative, whereas `ELTER NULL` and `ELSE NULL` are equivalent to `FILTER`.

**Three top-N-per-group patterns, all with exact output**

```sql
-- Pattern 1: window function (the one to default to)
WITH ranked AS (
  SELECT sale_id, region, product, amount,
         row_number() OVER (PARTITION BY region ORDER BY amount DESC, sale_id) AS rn
  FROM   sales
)
SELECT * FROM ranked WHERE rn <= 2 ORDER BY region, rn;
```
```
 sale_id | region | product | amount  | rn
---------+--------+---------+---------+----
       3 | East   | Gadget  |  200.00 |  1
       1 | East   | Widget  |  100.00 |  2
       4 | West   | Widget  |  300.00 |  1
       6 | West   | Widget  |  300.00 |  2
```
Four rows. With `rank()` instead of `row_number()` you would get **five** rows, because West's 300.00 tie gives both rows rank 1 and both are `<= 2`.

```sql
-- Pattern 2: DISTINCT ON (Postgres only; the least portable but very fast form)
SELECT DISTINCT ON (region) region, sale_id, product, amount
FROM   sales
ORDER  BY region, amount DESC, sale_id;
```
```
 region | sale_id | product | amount
--------+---------+---------+--------
 East   |       3 | Gadget  | 200.00
 West   |       4 | Widget  | 300.00
```
Two rows: one per region. `DISTINCT ON` needs the `ORDER BY` to start with the `DISTINCT ON` columns. Note it gives you exactly one arbitrary-ish row per group (picked by your `ORDER BY` tie-breaker), so it is for "oldest/newest per group", not for "top 3".

```sql
-- Pattern 3: correlated subquery with count (classic top-N via anti-join)
SELECT s.sale_id, s.region, s.product, s.amount
FROM   sales s
WHERE  (SELECT count(*) FROM sales x
        WHERE x.region = s.region AND x.amount > s.amount) < 2
ORDER  BY s.region, s.amount DESC, s.sale_id;
```
```
 sale_id | region | product | amount
---------+--------+---------+--------
       3 | East   | Gadget  | 200.00
       1 | East   | Widget  | 100.00
       2 | East   | Widget  | 100.00
       4 | West   | Widget  | 300.00
       6 | West   | Widget  | 300.00
```
Five rows: East gets all three because the tie at 100.00 means neither row has two rows strictly above it, and West gets only the two 300.00 rows because 150.00 has two rows above it. This is "fewer than 2 strictly greater" semantics — a fourth distinct formulation of top-N, and a good thing to be able to produce on a whiteboard.

```sql
-- Pattern 4 (bonus): LATERAL, the fastest when each region has an index
SELECT r.region, t.sale_id, t.product, t.amount
FROM   (SELECT DISTINCT region FROM sales) r
CROSS JOIN LATERAL (
  SELECT sale_id, product, amount FROM sales
  WHERE region = r.region ORDER BY amount DESC, sale_id LIMIT 2
) t
ORDER BY r.region, t.amount DESC, t.sale_id;
```
```
 region | sale_id | product | amount
--------+---------+---------+--------
 East   |       3 | Gadget  | 200.00
 East   |       1 | Widget  | 100.00
 West   |       4 | Widget  | 300.00
 West   |       6 | Widget  | 300.00
```

**Gaps and islands**

Islands = maximal runs of consecutive values; gaps = the missing values between them.

```sql
CREATE TABLE event_log (user_id integer NOT NULL, day date NOT NULL);
INSERT INTO event_log VALUES
  (1,'2024-01-01'),(1,'2024-01-02'),(1,'2024-01-03'),(1,'2024-01-06'),(1,'2024-01-07'),
  (2,'2024-01-01'),(2,'2024-01-10');

-- islands: subtract a per-partition row number from the value
WITH numbered AS (
  SELECT user_id, day,
         day - (row_number() OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY day)) AS island_key
  FROM   event_log
)
SELECT user_id, min(day) AS island_start, max(day) AS island_end, count(*) AS island_len
FROM   numbered
GROUP  BY user_id, island_key
ORDER  BY user_id, island_start;
```
```
 user_id | island_start | island_end | island_len
---------+--------------+------------+------------
       1 | 2024-01-01   | 2024-01-03 |          3
       1 | 2024-01-06   | 2024-01-07 |          2
       2 | 2024-01-01   | 2024-01-01 |          1
       2 | 2024-01-10   | 2024-01-10 |          1
```
`date - integer` is a `date` in Postgres, so consecutive dates share the same key; a one-day jump changes the key by one day, starting a new island.

Consecutive integer streaks use the identical trick on an integer:
```sql
WITH t(n) AS (VALUES (1),(2),(3),(5),(6),(9)),
 g AS (SELECT n, n - row_number() OVER (ORDER BY n) AS k FROM t)
SELECT min(n) AS run_start, max(n) AS run_end, count(*) AS run_len
FROM   g GROUP BY k ORDER BY k;
```
```
 run_start | run_end | run_len
-----------+---------+--------
         1 |       3 |       3
         5 |       6 |       2
         9 |       9 |       1
```

Find the *missing* values (the gaps) explicitly:
```sql
WITH t(n) AS (VALUES (1),(2),(3),(5),(6),(9)),
 g AS (SELECT n, n - row_number() OVER (ORDER BY n) AS k FROM t),
 islands AS (SELECT k, min(n) AS lo, max(n) AS hi FROM g GROUP BY k)
SELECT lo + generate_series(1, hi - lo - 1) AS missing FROM islands
WHERE hi - lo > 1
ORDER BY missing;
-- 4, 7, 8
```

**Streak detection: "3+ days in a row"**
```sql
WITH numbered AS (
  SELECT user_id, day,
         day - (row_number() OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY day)) AS k
  FROM   event_log
), islands AS (
  SELECT user_id, k, count(*) AS len FROM numbered GROUP BY user_id, k
)
SELECT * FROM islands WHERE len >= 3;
-- user 1, one island, len 3
```

**`HAVING` vs `WHERE` vs `FILTER`**

| Clause | When it runs | Sees | Can it use an index? | Aggregates allowed? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `WHERE` | Before grouping/sorting | Individual row values | Yes | No |
| `HAVING` | After grouping | Group values and aggregates | No (except via a planner trick with a functional-dependency group key) | Yes |
| `FILTER` | During aggregation | Individual row values | No | Yes, on the aggregate itself |

```sql
SELECT dept_id, count(*) AS n, avg(salary) AS avg_sal
FROM   emp
WHERE  salary IS NOT NULL          -- drops Leo before grouping
GROUP  BY dept_id
HAVING count(*) >= 2 AND avg(salary) > 5000
ORDER  BY dept_id;
```
```
 dept_id | n  | avg_sal
---------+----+---------
       1 |  4 | 7250.00     -- Ada 9000, Bob 7000, Cleo 7000, Jan 6500 => 29500/4
       3 |  2 | 5750.00     -- Gus 6000, Hal 5500
```
Engineering: 9000+7000+7000+6500 = 29500, /4 = 7250. Sales is excluded because its average is 4666.67. Moving `salary IS NOT NULL` to `HAVING` gives the same result here (NULLs are ignored by both aggregates), but it changes the plan: `WHERE` can use an index and reduces the rows before the sort/hash; `HAVING` forces the full group first. `WHERE salary > 6000` in `HAVING` would *not* be equivalent, because `HAVING` cannot filter individual rows — it can only filter groups, and a non-aggregate `salary` in `HAVING` is not standard (Postgres allows it, MySQL allows it, and both then behave unpredictably with grouped and ungrouped columns).

**The grouped-column rule**
```sql
-- Postgres: this is an error and should be
SELECT dept_id, name, count(*) FROM emp GROUP BY dept_id;
-- ERROR:  column "emp.name" must appear in the GROUP BY clause or be used in an aggregate function
-- MySQL (default, ONLY_FULL_GROUP_GROUP off) accepts it and returns an arbitrary row per group.
-- Fix: GROUP BY dept_id, name  |  aggregate it: max(name)  |  use min/max() to pick deterministically
```

**Questions**
1. What is the difference between `DISTINCT`, `GROUP BY`, and a window function's `PARTITION BY`?
2. Why can a window function not appear in `WHERE`?
3. What is the default frame, and what does "peer" mean?
4. Compute `RANK` and `DENSE_RANK` for West on the fixture. (1,1,3 and 1,1,2.)
5. Why does `row_number()` need a tie-breaker?
6. Why does `last_value()` need an explicit frame and `first_value()` not?
7. Write a running total with the default frame and explain the tie behaviour.
8. When do you use `ROWS` over `RANGE`?
9. `FILTER` vs `CASE WHEN` inside an aggregate: which handles negative values better, and why?
10. Write the top-2-per-region query in three different ways.
11. What does `DISTINCT ON` require, and which engine lacks it?
12. Explain the islands-and-gaps trick. Why does `value - row_number()` work?
13. `WHERE` vs `HAVING` vs `FILTER`: when does each run, and which is index-friendly?
14. What is a named window and when does it help?
15. What does `lag(amount, 1, 0)` do and why would you use it?
16. When is a correlated subquery *not* decorrelated? (When it has `LIMIT`, `DISTINCT`, or an aggregate that would change cardinality.)
17. Postgres 12+ inlines a single-use CTE. How can that be a performance problem?
18. How does a recursive CTE terminate? What happens with a cyclic graph?
19. MySQL: what is `max_recursion_depth` and why does it bite in production?
20. `PERCENT_RANK` vs `CUME_DIST`: formulas and one use each.

**Model answer, Q6:** "Context: I was computing 'days since the last login' for a cohort chart and got zeros for most users. Decision: always write the frame explicitly. Reasoning: when a window has an `ORDER BY`, the SQL standard sets the default frame to `RANGE BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW`, which is row-value based, not row-position based. `RANGE` extends the frame through the last row that is a peer of the current row, so if ten users logged in on the same day, each of their running sums includes all ten. `last_value` then returns the current row's value, because the frame ends at the current row, and `first_value` looks correct only because the frame always starts at the partition's beginning. So `ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND UNBOUNDED FOLLOWING` is the only way to say 'the whole partition', and it is what I write for `last_value`, `nth_value`, and any whole-group aggregate. Failure scenario: a rolling 7-day metric silently computed over fewer than seven days of data whenever several events shared a timestamp, so the chart had flat segments nobody could explain. Prevention: treat an unstated frame as a bug; make the frame explicit in every window function you write, and add the unique tie-breaker to every `ORDER BY` inside a window."

**Mistakes:** trusting `row_number()` without a tie-breaker; assuming the window's `ORDER BY` controls output order; `last_value` with the default frame; `WHERE` after a `GROUP BY` when you meant `HAVING` (or vice versa); assuming `WITH` creates a temp table; `NOT IN` inside a CTE; `GROUP BY` on a nullable column and expecting NULL groups to separate; relying on `ctid` or physical order in a result.

**Mini task:** with the `sales` fixture, produce a single query that returns for each region: total, running total by sale_id, 3-row moving average, rank, dense rank, percent rank, cume dist, lag, lead, first and last value, and the row's position in NTILE(4) of its region. Verify every number against the tables above. Then add a tie (a fourth East row at 100.00) and explain exactly which columns change and why.

---

# 6.5 Transactions, ACID, and Isolation

Intermediate to Advanced · **Must-know** (this section is the difference between a mid and a senior backend answer)

## 6.5.1 Transaction Control

Postgres runs in **autocommit**: every statement is its own transaction unless you say otherwise. MySQL's default `autocommit` is also on.

```sql
BEGIN;                      -- start; Postgres accepts BEGIN or START TRANSACTION
SAVEPOINT sp1;              -- nested rollback point
INSERT INTO audit (...) VALUES (...);
SAVEPOINT sp2;
UPDATE account SET balance = balance - 100 WHERE id = 1;
ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT sp2;  -- only the update is undone; work after sp1 survives
COMMIT;                     -- sp1's work is durable
-- COMMIT AND CHAIN / ROLLBACK AND CHAIN: start a new transaction immediately,
-- which is convenient for interactive work and keeps isolation level settings
```

```sql
-- Postgres equivalents of MySQL's START TRANSACTION WITH CONSISTENT SNAPSHOT
BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ;
BEGIN TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE READ ONLY DEFERRABLE;
-- SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE;  -- must be before any query in the tx
-- SET LOCAL lock_timeout = '2s';                 -- session only, resets at commit
```

**The rules that actually bite**

1. **A transaction is a unit of atomicity, not a unit of work.** Keep it as short as possible: open it, do the database work, commit.
2. **Never make a network call inside a transaction.** Holding a transaction open across an HTTP request to a payment provider, an LDAP/SSO lookup, an S3 call, or a `sleep()` holds locks, pins an MVCC snapshot, and blocks vacuum. The pool connection is held too. The correct shape is: commit the state change and write an outbox row in one short transaction, then make the call outside it (see 6.2.2).
3. **Read-only long transactions are the worst kind.** They pin `xmin`, so dead tuples can never be reclaimed and the table bloats until the transaction ends (see 6.8 vacuum).
4. **`COMMIT` is not `fsync`.** With `synchronous_commit = off`, commit returns before the WAL is on disk. With the default, commit waits for the WAL flush but not for the checkpoint.
5. **DDL in Postgres is transactional.** A failed `ALTER TABLE` rolls back completely. MySQL DDL is *not* transactional: a failed `ALTER TABLE` leaves the schema half-changed and cannot be rolled back. This single difference changes how you plan migrations.
6. **Savepoints cost.** Each savepoint is a marker; deeply nested savepoints add bookkeeping on every statement and bloat the transaction. Use one or two.
7. **Statement-level atomicity, not statement-level safety.** If a single `INSERT ... SELECT` inserts 10M rows and fails on row 9,999,999, the whole statement is rolled back. There is no partial statement.

## 6.5.2 What Each ACID Property Actually Guarantees

| Property | Marketing meaning | What actually happens at the storage layer | How it can still be violated |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Atomicity** | All or nothing | Every tuple change carries an `xmin`; the transaction's commit is recorded in the WAL (a "commit record"); visibility is decided by whether that record is present. Uncommitted changes live in the transaction's private buffer set and are invisible to everyone else | Only if the application writes outside the transaction (a file, another service, a cache). ACID does not cover side effects |
| **Consistency** | Constraints always hold | Provided by `CHECK`, `NOT NULL`, `UNIQUE`, FK triggers, and the type system, enforced on every write path | Only if constraints are missing or disabled (`session_replication_role = replica` disables FK triggers; `ALTER TABLE ... DISABLE TRIGGER`; MySQL `SET foreign_key_checks = 0`) |
| **Isolation** | Transactions do not see each other's partial work | MVCC: each transaction gets a snapshot of which tuples are visible (`xmin`/`xmax` rules); conflicts are handled with tuple locks plus row-level locks for writers | It is not the same as serial execution. Snapshot isolation admits **write skew** and **phantoms** unless the level is `SERIALIZABLE` (see below) |
| **Durability** | Committed data survives a crash | WAL is written and (with `synchronous_commit = on`) flushed before `COMMIT` returns. On restart, recovery replays the WAL from the last checkpoint | Power loss with `synchronous_commit = off`; a failed disk array that lied about flushing; a filesystem-level corruption; loss of the WAL archive if you needed it for PITR (6.8) |

The senior correction people miss: **ACID's "C" is a property of the schema, not of the transaction.** Adding a `CHECK` constraint in a later release breaks the consistency guarantees of transactions that were fine before. And **"I" is not "serializable"** in most default configurations — Postgres default `READ COMMITTED` is snapshot isolation and permits anomalies that would be impossible under true serial execution.

## 6.5.3 The Isolation-Level Matrix

| Anomaly | READ UNCOMMITTED | READ COMMITTED | REPEATABLE READ | SERIALIZABLE |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Dirty read** (read uncommitted data) | Possible | Prevented | Prevented | Prevented |
| **Non-repeatable read** (same row, different value on re-read) | Possible | Possible | Prevented | Prevented |
| **Phantom read** (new rows appear in a re-run predicate) | Possible | Possible | Prevented in MySQL; **possible in Postgres RR** (snapshot is fixed, but a new row inserted and committed by another transaction is invisible — however Postgres RR does not *guarantee* phantom-freedom for serializable purposes) | Prevented (in the sense that the serializable order exists; the transaction may be aborted with `40001`) |
| **Write skew** (each transaction's write is valid in isolation, together they violate an invariant) | Possible | **Possible** | **Possible** in Postgres | Prevented (aborted with `40001`) |
| **Lost update** (read-modify-write in application code) | Possible | **Possible** | **Possible** | Prevented |
| **Read/write skew, black-white problem** | Possible | Possible | Possible | Prevented |
| Postgres default? | No (treated as RC) | **Yes** | No | No |
| MySQL InnoDB default? | No | No | **Yes** | No |
| Implementation | Ignored by Postgres | New snapshot per statement | Same snapshot for the whole transaction (MySQL uses a consistent read view + next-key/gap locks) | PostgreSQL Serializable Snapshot Isolation (SSI): `SIREAD` predicate locks + conflict detection. MySQL: all rows read take shared locks (pessimistic) |
| Cost | Free | Low | Low (Postgres), moderate (MySQL locks) | High: more aborts, more predicate-lock bookkeeping |

**Two-transaction timelines.** In each, T1 is committed to a read then a write; T2 does the same; `R`/`W` are shown at their logical moment.

**Dirty read** (only possible under `READ UNCOMMITTED`)
```
Time  T1                              T2
 1   BEGIN;
 2   UPDATE account SET bal = 0        BEGIN;
 3    WHERE id = 1;                    SELECT bal FROM account WHERE id = 1;
 4                                    -- reads 0  <-- DIRTY
 5   ROLLBACK;
 6                                    -- T2 now believes the balance is 0.
```
Postgres and MySQL both raise this to READ COMMITTED or higher, so this timeline is a teaching device, not a live bug (except through a misconfigured proxy or a `READ UNCOMMITTED` transaction on another engine).

**Non-repeatable read**
```
 1   BEGIN;                           BEGIN;
 2   SELECT bal FROM account           SELECT bal FROM account
 3    WHERE id = 1;  --> 100            WHERE id = 1;  --> 100
 4                                    UPDATE account SET bal = 90 WHERE id = 1;
 5                                    COMMIT;
 6   SELECT bal FROM account
 7    WHERE id = 1;  --> 90    <-- different value for the same row
 8   COMMIT;
```
Prevented by REPEATABLE READ and above. Fixed either by the isolation level or, more explicitly and portably, with `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` on the rows you are about to modify.

**Phantom read**
```
 1   BEGIN;                           BEGIN;
 2   SELECT count(*) FROM orders       SELECT count(*) FROM orders
 3    WHERE status='pending'; --> 10    WHERE status='pending'; --> 10
 4                                    INSERT INTO orders (...) VALUES (..., 'pending');
 5                                    COMMIT;
 6   SELECT count(*) FROM orders
 7    WHERE status='pending'; --> 11   <-- new row appeared
```
Under Postgres `REPEATABLE READ` the transaction's snapshot is fixed, so row 6 returns 10 — the phantom is invisible. Under MySQL RR the same query returns 10 as well, because InnoDB takes gap and next-key locks on the scanned range so the other insert blocks. Both still allow write skew, which is the anomaly that actually bites.

**Write skew** — the one worth being able to draw from memory
```sql
CREATE TABLE on_call (
  doctor_id integer NOT NULL,
  day       date    NOT NULL,
  on_call   boolean NOT NULL,
  PRIMARY KEY (doctor_id, day)
);
-- App invariant (enforced only in application code): at least one doctor is on call per day.
```
```
 1   BEGIN;                              BEGIN;
 2   SELECT count(*) FROM on_call        SELECT count(*) FROM on_call
 3    WHERE day='2024-03-01'              WHERE day='2024-03-01'
 4      AND on_call;  --> 1               AND on_call;  --> 1
 5   -- I am safe to take the day off    -- I am safe to take the day off
 6   UPDATE on_call SET on_call=false    UPDATE on_call SET on_call=false
 7    WHERE doctor_id=1                   WHERE doctor_id=2
 8      AND day='2024-03-01';             AND day='2024-03-01';
 9   COMMIT;                            COMMIT;
10   Result: ZERO doctors on call. Both transactions' reads were valid
11   snapshots and both writes touched disjoint rows. No lock conflict occurred.
```
Every transaction behaves exactly as if it had run alone, and the combination is illegal. This is the definition of write skew, and it is *not* fixed by REPEATABLE READ in Postgres. Fixes, in order of preference:
1. **Model the invariant in the schema**: a `booster`/covering table with `CHECK (EXISTS (SELECT ...))`, or a total-count row that both transactions must update (now they conflict on the same row).
2. **`SERIALIZABLE`**: Postgres's SSI aborts one transaction with `40001`; you retry.
3. **Serialize on a shared row**: `SELECT ... FROM on_call_day_lock WHERE day = $1 FOR UPDATE` — both transactions queue on the same row.
4. **Advisory lock**: `pg_advisory_xact_lock(hashtext('on_call'), day)`.
5. `pg_advisory_xact_lock` released at commit (see 6.6 for the pooling trap).

**Lost update**
```
 1   BEGIN;                                BEGIN;
 2   SELECT bal FROM account                SELECT bal FROM account
 3    WHERE id=1;  --> 100                    WHERE id=1;  --> 100
 4   -- app computes 100-10                 -- app computes 100-20
 5   UPDATE account SET bal = 90             UPDATE account SET bal = 80
 6    WHERE id=1;                            WHERE id=1;
 7   COMMIT;                              COMMIT;
 8   Final balance 80. It should be 70: one update was lost.
```
Important accuracy note: Postgres and MySQL both re-read the latest row version when *evaluating* an update, so `UPDATE account SET bal = bal - 10` in a transaction under `READ COMMITTED` or `REPEATABLE READ` is **not** lost-update-prone — the second writer blocks on the row lock, then re-evaluates against the newer version (Postgres calls this EvalPlanQual; MySQL calls it the current read). The lost update happens when the value is computed in the *application* and written as an absolute value, which is exactly what an ORM does with `SET bal = :newValue` after a read. Fix: compute in the SQL (`bal = bal - :n`), or use `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` before the read-modify-write, or an optimistic version column.

## 6.5.4 Postgres `READ COMMITTED` Semantics

`READ COMMITTED` (the Postgres default) takes a **new snapshot at the start of every statement**, not at `BEGIN`. Within one transaction, two `SELECT`s of the same rows can see different data. This is deliberate: it minimizes blocking, and in 9.0+ it is implemented by *re-checking* the row after a lock wait rather than by blocking readers (EvalPlanQual):

```
T1                                  T2
BEGIN;                              BEGIN;
UPDATE account SET bal = bal - 10   SELECT bal FROM account WHERE id = 1;
 WHERE id = 1;                      -- sees 100 (T1 uncommitted, invisible)
-- locks row 1
                                   UPDATE account SET bal = bal - 20 WHERE id = 1;
                                    -- T2 blocks on the row lock
COMMIT;
                                    -- T2 wakes up, sees 90, sets 70. Correct.
```

So under `READ COMMITTED`: writers block writers on the same row (no lost arithmetic updates), readers never block and never see uncommitted data, and everything else is fair game. When you need "the rows I read 10 minutes ago are the same rows now", you must ask for `REPEATABLE READ` explicitly.

**`REPEATABLE READ` in Postgres** = snapshot fixed at the first statement of the transaction, plus SSI-lite. Consequences: the transaction can see a consistent view for its whole life (good for long reports), but it also pins `xmin` for its whole life (bad for vacuum — see 6.8), and two `REPEATABLE READ` transactions writing disjoint rows can still produce write skew.

## 6.5.5 `SERIALIZABLE` and the Mandatory Retry Loop

Under `SERIALIZABLE`, Postgres may abort your transaction at any point with:

```
ERROR:  could not serialize access due to read/write dependencies among transactions
SQLSTATE: 40001
```

This is **not** an error condition; it is the isolation level telling you "your transaction's assumptions no longer hold, run it again." The application must implement retry with backoff, and the retry must be safe (the whole transaction, from `BEGIN`, including re-reading the inputs).

```sql
-- server side
SET default_transaction_isolation = 'serializable';
-- or per transaction:
BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE;
SET LOCAL statement_timeout = '5s';
...
COMMIT;
```

```ts
// application side: retry the WHOLE transaction on 40001 / 40P01
const SERIALIZATION_FAILURE = '40001';
const DEADLOCK_DETECTED     = '40P01';

async function inTransaction<T>(fn: (c: PoolClient) => Promise<T>, attempts = 5): Promise<T> {
  for (let i = 0; ; i++) {
    const client = await pool.connect();
    try {
      await client.query('BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE');
      const out = await fn(client);
      await client.query('COMMIT');
      return out;
    } catch (err) {
      await client.query('ROLLBACK').catch(() => {});
      const code = (err as { code?: string }).code;
      if ((code === SERIALIZATION_FAILURE || code === DEADLOCK_DETECTED) && i < attempts) {
        await sleep(Math.random() * 50 * 2 ** i);   // full jitter
        continue;
      }
      throw err;
    } finally {
      client.release();
    }
  }
}
```

Retry rules: exponential backoff with jitter (two workers that deadlock will deadlock again if they retry in lockstep), cap the attempts, log the count, alert if it climbs, and make the function idempotent or re-read its inputs on retry. Also: use `SET LOCAL` for timeouts inside the transaction so a stuck query cannot hold the snapshot forever.

**When to choose `SERIALIZABLE`:** inventory reservations, seat/ticket booking, balance transfers with an invariant, quota enforcement, and any "check-then-act" invariant that cannot be modelled as a constraint. Use `pg_advisory_xact_lock` or an explicit row lock when the contention is on a single well-known row, because `SERIALIZABLE` aborts are a function of contention and can get expensive in a hot loop. Monitor `pg_stat_database.deadlocks` and the `40001` rate.

## 6.5.6 Long Transactions Block Vacuum

Postgres MVCC stores every row version. A dead tuple can be removed only when **no** running transaction might still need it, i.e. when every transaction older than the tuple is finished. A transaction that began an hour ago and is still open therefore freezes the vacuum horizon for every table it has touched.

```
T1: BEGIN; SELECT count(*) FROM orders;   -- takes a snapshot at 09:00
... (T1 keeps the transaction open, e.g. a long report or a buggy code path)
    Autovacuum cannot remove dead tuples created after 09:00.
    orders grows 500M rows/day => dead-tuple bloat accumulates without bound,
    index scans degrade, disk fills, and eventually an emergency VACUUM is needed
    that cannot even finish because T1 is still open.
```

Detection: `SELECT pid, state, xact_start, query FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE xact_start IS NOT NULL AND now() - xact_start > interval '5 minutes' ORDER BY xact_start;`. Prevention: `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout`, `statement_timeout`, `lock_timeout`, and `idle_session_timeout`, all set per role so a forgotten transaction cannot outlive a request. Never run interactive analysis inside a long-open transaction on a production database.

## 6.5.7 Why Network Calls Must Never Be Inside a Transaction

Concrete failure, no speculation needed:
1. `BEGIN; UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - 100 WHERE id = 1;`
2. Call the payment provider. It is slow, or you built it to retry, or the network drops.
3. The transaction holds a row lock on `accounts` for the whole call. Every concurrent transfer touching that account queues behind it. Your connection pool's connections are all pinned. The provider call eventually times out at 30s, so the lock was held for 30s.
4. If the provider call succeeded but the `COMMIT` then fails (or the process is killed between the provider's response and the commit), you have **money moved with no local record**: the classic dual-write problem. There is no distributed transaction across the provider's API.
5. Meanwhile autovacuum cannot advance past the transaction's snapshot, so a payment page that is merely slow becomes a table-bloat incident.

Correct shape:
```sql
BEGIN;
UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - 100 WHERE id = 1;
INSERT INTO payment_intent (account_id, amount, state) VALUES (1, 100, 'pending');
INSERT INTO outbox (topic, payload) VALUES ('payment.requested', '{"intent_id":...}');
COMMIT;
-- outside the transaction: a worker picks up the outbox row, calls the provider,
-- retries with backoff, and is idempotent on intent_id
```
The transaction is short (single-digit milliseconds), the network call is outside it, the intent is durable, and the state machine (`pending` → `succeeded`/`failed`) is explicit so a lost response can be reconciled by polling the provider rather than by guessing.

**Model answer, Q4 (write skew):** "Context: two doctors can each take the same on-call day off. The invariant is 'at least one doctor is on call per day' and it is only enforced in application code. Both doctors open a transaction, each reads the count of on-call doctors as 1, each concludes it is safe to take the day off, each updates their own row, and both commit. No row was contended, so no lock or abort happened, and the final state violates the invariant. That is write skew: every transaction behaved as if it ran alone, and the interleaving of legal operations produced an illegal state. Decision: fix the model, not just the symptom. Option one is to make the invariant part of the schema, for example a `on_call_day` row per day that both transactions must lock or increment, so they contend and serialize. Option two is `SERIALIZABLE`, which makes Postgres SSI abort one of them with SQLSTATE 40001, and I implement a retry loop around the whole transaction. Option three is an explicit advisory lock per day. Trade-offs: SERIALIZABLE gives correctness everywhere but the abort rate scales with contention, so a hot key would cause a retry storm; an explicit lock is cheap and predictable but only protects code that remembers to take it. Failure scenario: the day arrives with nobody on call and the invariant is discovered by a phone call, because nothing in the system ever reported an error. Prevention: any invariant that spans rows must be a constraint, a lock, or a `SERIALIZABLE` transaction; write it down in the schema documentation next to the table."

**Questions**
1. What does autocommit do to a multi-statement unit of work?
2. What is `SAVEPOINT` for, and when is it used?
3. Which ACID property is really a property of the *schema*? (Consistency.)
4. What does durability actually require at commit time in Postgres?
5. Give the dirty-read timeline. Which default level prevents it?
6. Non-repeatable read vs phantom read: give a one-line distinguishing example for each.
7. Walk through write skew. Why does `REPEATABLE READ` not stop it in Postgres?
8. Three ways to prevent write skew?
9. What exactly does a Postgres `READ COMMITTED` snapshot cover?
10. Why is `UPDATE t SET n = n - 1` safe from lost updates in Postgres but `SET n = :computed` is not?
11. What does SQLSTATE `40001` mean and what must the application do?
12. Full jitter versus fixed backoff for retry, and why?
13. Which isolation level does each engine default to, and how would that change a bug you debug?
14. Why do long transactions hurt vacuum?
15. What is the dual-write problem and what is the outbox pattern?
16. Name three things that must never be inside a transaction.
17. How do you detect and kill long-running transactions in production?
18. When is `REPEATABLE READ` the right choice in Postgres despite the vacuum cost?
19. What happens to `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` under `READ COMMITTED` if the row was updated and committed while you waited?
20. How do you make a transaction idempotent for retry?

**Model answer, Q11:** "`40001` is SQLSTATE `serialization_failure`. Postgres's Serializable Snapshot Isolation detects a cycle of read/write dependencies among concurrent transactions and aborts the one it thinks is cheapest, because the transaction's snapshot no longer supports a serial execution that matches what it read. It is not a bug and not a transient infrastructure failure; it is the isolation level reporting that my assumptions were wrong. So the application must treat it exactly like a deadlock: roll back the whole transaction and start over, with exponential backoff and jitter to avoid two workers re-colliding in lockstep, up to a bounded number of attempts. It must be safe to retry, which means the function either is idempotent or re-reads its inputs on the second attempt, because the world may have changed. Trade-offs are that the abort rate rises with contention, so I would not put a hot contended invariant under `SERIALIZABLE` without measuring; I would also alert on the rate, because a jump means either contention changed or a client stopped retrying."

**Mistakes:** assuming ACID covers non-database side effects; opening a transaction at the start of a request handler and committing at the end (which includes network I/O); retrying only the failed statement instead of the whole transaction; fixed-interval retries; believing `REPEATABLE READ` prevents write skew in Postgres; leaving `idle in transaction` sessions around; calling a `40001` "a database outage".

**Mini task:** write a script with two psql sessions and a scheduler that reproduces, in order: dirty read (using `SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL READ UNCOMMITTED` on a non-Postgres engine, or explaining why Postgres cannot show it), non-repeatable read, phantom, lost update, and write skew. For each, print the exact level required to prevent it. Then flip the app to `SERIALIZABLE`, run 50 concurrent inventory reservations for 10 units, and plot aborts vs retries.

---

# 6.6 Locking and Deadlocks

Advanced · **Must-know** for backend roles; **Good-to-know** for full stack

## 6.6.1 Row Locks vs Table Locks

Postgres (and InnoDB) use MVCC: readers never block writers and writers never block readers. Locks matter for **writers**, and for DDL.

| Lock | Scope | Conflicts with | Held until | Typical trigger |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `FOR KEY SHARE` | Row | `FOR UPDATE` (on the same key) | End of transaction | Foreign-key check on the referenced row; `SELECT ... FOR KEY SHARE` |
| `FOR SHARE` / `FOR SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE`(PG) | Row | `FOR NO KEY UPDATE`, `FOR UPDATE`, `FOR EXCLUSIVE` | End of transaction | `SELECT ... FOR SHARE` |
| `FOR NO KEY UPDATE` | Row | `FOR SHARE`, `FOR NO KEY UPDATE`, `FOR UPDATE` | End of transaction | `UPDATE` of a non-key column; FK checks on the referencing side |
| `FOR UPDATE` | Row | All other row lock modes | End of transaction | `UPDATE` of a key column, `DELETE`, `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` |
| `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` | Row | — (skips locked rows) | — | Job queues |
| Table `ACCESS SHARE` | Table | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` | End of statement | Any `SELECT` |
| Table `ROW SHARE` | Table | `EXCLUSIVE`, `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` | End of transaction | `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE/SHARE`, `UPDATE`, `DELETE` |
| Table `ROW EXCLUSIVE` | Table | `SHARE`, `EXCLUSIVE`, `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` | End of transaction | `UPDATE`, `INSERT`, `DELETE` |
| Table `SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE` | Table | `SHARE`, `SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE`, `SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE`, `EXCLUSIVE`, `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` | End of transaction | `VACUUM`, `ANALYZE`, `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY`, `ALTER TABLE ... VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` |
| Table `SHARE` | Table | `ROW EXCLUSIVE` and above | End of transaction | `CREATE INDEX` (non-concurrent) |
| Table `EXCLUSIVE` | Table | `ROW SHARE` and above | End of transaction | `REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW CONCURRENTLY` |
| Table `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` | Table | Everything | End of transaction | `ALTER TABLE`, `DROP`, `TRUNCATE`, `REINDEX`, `VACUUM FULL`, `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` on the table in some cases, adding an FK |

The only lock mode that is essentially always safe is `ACCESS SHARE` (a plain `SELECT`). `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` blocks *all* access including reads, which is why a plain `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN ... DEFAULT` on a large table can take down a live site — see 6.13 for the zero-downtime recipe.

InnoDB adds **intent locks**: before taking a row lock, the transaction records an `IS`/`IX` table-level intent lock so any table-level lock conflict can be detected without scanning rows. It also has **gap and next-key locks** under `REPEATABLE READ` for range scans, which is why a plain `SELECT ... WHERE id BETWEEN 1 AND 10 FOR UPDATE` can block an `INSERT` of id 7 from another transaction (see 6.9).

## 6.6.2 The Four `FOR` Modes and a Conflict Matrix

Conflict matrix (symmetric; "X" means the modes conflict):

| Requested \ Held | KEY SHARE | SHARE | NO KEY UPDATE | UPDATE |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **KEY SHARE** | — | — | — | X |
| **SHARE** | — | — | X | X |
| **NO KEY UPDATE** | — | X | X | X |
| **UPDATE** | X | X | X | X |

```sql
SELECT ... FOR KEY SHARE;        -- FK-safe read; many readers, blocks key updates
SELECT ... FOR SHARE;            -- "read and don't let anyone change it"
SELECT ... FOR NO KEY UPDATE;    -- like FOR UPDATE but weaker; used internally
SELECT ... FOR UPDATE;           -- exclusive row lock
SELECT ... FOR UPDATE OF e;      -- lock only the e rows (PG 9.3+)
SELECT ... FOR UPDATE NOWAIT;    -- error 55P03 immediately if locked
SELECT ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED; -- silently skip locked rows
```

Rules:
- **Locks are released at `COMMIT`/`ROLLBACK`**, never at the end of the statement (except the implicit transaction from a single autocommit statement). So `BEGIN; SELECT ... FOR UPDATE; COMMIT;` holds the lock only until the commit; `BEGIN; ... many statements ...; COMMIT;` holds it for all of them.
- **An implicit transaction from `FOR UPDATE` in autocommit mode ends immediately**, so `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` without an explicit `BEGIN` is a no-op in effect. Always pair with `BEGIN`.
- **Subqueries take their own locks**: `SELECT ... FROM t WHERE id IN (SELECT id FROM u FOR UPDATE)` locks `u` rows.
- Lock escalation does not exist in Postgres (unlike SQL Server); you take row locks and one table lock, and there is no "table has 5000 locks, escalate" cliff. That is a genuine advantage worth stating.
- `FOR UPDATE` cannot be used with `GROUP BY`, aggregates, `DISTINCT`, `UNION`, or `HAVING` — put it in a subquery or use `LATERAL`.

## 6.6.3 `NOWAIT` and `SKIP LOCKED`

```sql
-- fail fast instead of blocking
BEGIN;
SELECT * FROM seat WHERE seat_id = $1 FOR UPDATE NOWAIT;
-- ERROR:  could not obtain lock on row in relation "seat"   SQLSTATE 55P03
-- Pair with lock_timeout for a soft version:
SET LOCAL lock_timeout = '200ms';
```

**The job-queue pattern.** A single `UPDATE ... SKIP LOCKED` gives you a work queue with no extra infrastructure, no `SELECT FOR UPDATE` then `UPDATE` race, and no advisory locks:

```sql
-- dequeue: each worker grabs distinct rows, nobody blocks anybody
UPDATE job SET
  status      = 'running',
  locked_by   = current_setting('application_name'),
  locked_at   = now(),
  attempts    = attempts + 1
WHERE ctid IN (
  SELECT ctid FROM job
  WHERE status = 'pending'
    AND run_at <= now()
  ORDER BY run_at
  FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED
  LIMIT 5
)
RETURNING *;
```
`RETURNING` gives you exactly the rows you claimed. This is the standard Postgres job queue, and it is what `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` exists for. Caveats: ordering by `run_at` only is a starvation risk (add a tie-breaker), there is no visibility timeout by default (add one: reclaim `running` rows where `locked_at < now() - interval '5 minutes'`), and if you use `SKIP LOCKED` with `LIMIT 1` in a loop you will hot-spot on the head of the queue.

`SKIP LOCKED` is also the trick behind "each of N parallel workers takes a disjoint slice of a table":
```sql
-- 8 parallel workers, each reads a disjoint 1/8
SELECT * FROM big_table
WHERE ctid >= $lo AND ctid < $hi
```
or, more portably, partition the table and have each worker own a partition.

**Model answer, Q6 (queue design):** "Context: we need to run 50 million background jobs, some of which take minutes, with at-least-once delivery and no extra infrastructure. Decision: a Postgres table used as a queue, claimed with `UPDATE ... WHERE ctid IN (SELECT ctid FROM job WHERE ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED LIMIT n) RETURNING *`. Reasoning: `SKIP LOCKED` means concurrent workers take disjoint rows instead of queueing behind each other, and because the claim is a single atomic `UPDATE` there is no window between 'read the job' and 'mark it taken', which is the race that makes hand-rolled `SELECT` then `UPDATE` queues lose jobs. Trade-offs: it is a queue built on a b-tree, so a huge pending table means index pressure, `ON CONFLICT` contention, and vacuum on a hot table, and there is no built-in delivery guarantee or delayed-message support the way Kafka or SQS have. Failure scenario: a worker is `SIGKILL`ed after claiming a job, and because the row is `running` with no timeout, the job is stuck forever and the throughput quietly drops while nothing errors. Prevention: a `locked_at` heartbeat plus a reaper that resets rows whose `locked_at` is older than the timeout, plus an `attempts` counter and a dead-letter threshold; and I would migrate to a real broker once the backlog or the fan-out justifies it."

**Questions**
1. Which four row lock modes does Postgres offer, and which conflict with which?
2. Do Postgres locks escalate like SQL Server's? (No.)
3. When is a row lock released? (At commit or rollback.)
4. Why is `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` without `BEGIN` almost useless?
5. `FOR UPDATE NOWAIT`: which SQLSTATE, and when is it better than a timeout?
6. `lock_timeout` vs `statement_timeout` vs `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout`: which protects against what?
7. Write a single-statement job-claim with `SKIP LOCKED` and `RETURNING`. What does it guarantee?
8. Why does `SKIP LOCKED` avoid the "claim then crash" and "double claim" races?
9. What happens to a `SKIP LOCKED` queue if a worker dies mid-job? (Nothing automatic; you need a visibility timeout.)
10. `FOR UPDATE` with `GROUP BY`: what does Postgres say, and what are the alternatives?
11. What is a gap lock and why does MySQL need it?
12. What lock does `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN` take? What does `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` take?
13. Read the transaction's own locks: what view gives this? (`pg_locks` joined with `pg_stat_activity`.)
14. How do you lock an ordered set of rows to avoid deadlock? (`SELECT ... WHERE id IN (...) ORDER BY id FOR UPDATE`.)

**Mistakes:** `FOR UPDATE` in autocommit; assuming `SKIP LOCKED` gives exactly-once; a hand-rolled `SELECT` then `UPDATE` queue with a race; taking row locks in application order rather than key order; a single global advisory lock as a scaling strategy.

---

## 6.6.3 Advisory Locks and the Connection-Pooling Trap

Advisory locks are application-level mutexes that do not touch any table. They are perfect for "one cron per cluster" or "one cache rebuild at a time", and they are a foot-gun with pooling.

```sql
-- Session-level: released only when the session ends (or pg_advisory_unlock_all())
SELECT pg_advisory_lock(4242);        -- waits
SELECT pg_try_advisory_lock(4242);    -- returns false immediately if held
SELECT pg_advisory_unlock(4242);

-- Transaction-level: released at COMMIT or ROLLBACK automatically (much safer)
SELECT pg_advisory_xact_lock(4242);           -- waits for the lock, held until tx end
SELECT pg_try_advisory_xact_lock(4242);       -- boolean, no waiting
```

Two key spaces: one `bigint` (first overload) or two `int`s, and a shared vs exclusive variant: `pg_advisory_lock_shared` / `pg_advisory_lock`. The two-argument form is the good one because you can namespace by resource id: `pg_advisory_xact_lock(hashtext('rebuild_customer_cache'), customer_id)` — note `hashtext` returns `int4`, so the two-argument form is required.

**The pooling trap.** A session-level advisory lock is owned by the *connection*, not by your code. With PgBouncer in **transaction pooling mode**, the next query from that logical client may be served by a **different backend connection**, where the lock does not exist. Consequences:

```sql
-- Application (behind PgBouncer transaction pooling):
SELECT pg_try_advisory_lock(4242);  -- true, connection A now holds it
-- ... unrelated queries ...
SELECT pg_try_advisory_unlock(4242); -- "false: not owner" -- or worse, succeeds on connection B
```
Two workers both believe they hold the lock, and the "release" may fail or release someone else's. **With transaction pooling you must use `pg_advisory_xact_lock` (transaction-scoped), because the lock and the release are then the same transaction on the same backend connection.** Even that requires the lock and the work to be in one transaction — which conflicts with the "never hold a transaction across network calls" rule, so a session-level advisory lock or a proper queue table is usually the better answer for long-running work.

Postgres also has **transaction-level advisory locks used as a poor man's queue lock**, and `pg_try_advisory_xact_lock` in a loop, which is a known anti-pattern (it burns CPU and does not give fairness).

## 6.6.4 Optimistic vs Pessimistic Locking

**Pessimistic:** take the lock first, then read, then decide.
```sql
BEGIN;
SELECT balance FROM account WHERE id = $1 FOR UPDATE;   -- lock first
-- now safe to read-modify-write; other writers block
UPDATE account SET balance = $2 WHERE id = $1;
COMMIT;
```
- **Use when**: conflicts are frequent, the critical section is short, and correctness matters more than throughput (inventory decrement, seat booking).
- **Cost**: lock held for the whole transaction; blocks others; needs `NOWAIT`/`lock_timeout` handling; increases deadlock risk.

**Optimistic:** read without a lock, include a version, let the write fail.
```sql
CREATE TABLE account (
  account_id bigint PRIMARY KEY,
  balance    numeric(14,2) NOT NULL,
  version    integer NOT NULL DEFAULT 0
);

BEGIN;
SELECT balance, version FROM account WHERE account_id = $1;   -- no lock
-- application computes new balance = 90, version 0
UPDATE account
SET balance = 90, version = version + 1
WHERE account_id = $1 AND version = 0;                       -- compare-and-swap
-- 0 rows affected => someone else won. Retry (re-read, recompute) or return 409.
COMMIT;
```
