- **Use when**: conflicts are rare (most row updates touch different rows), the transaction is long, or you are sharded/distributed and cannot take a DB lock cheaply.
- **Cost**: retry logic on every conflict; the update can silently affect 0 rows, which the application must *check*; version-column maintenance in every ORM.

**Auto-increment `version` via trigger** so you never forget it:
```sql
CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION bump_version() RETURNS trigger LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
BEGIN
  NEW.version := OLD.version + 1;
  RETURN NEW;
END $$;

CREATE TRIGGER account_bump BEFORE UPDATE ON account
  FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION bump_version();
```

**At scale**, the optimistic variant is what survives: two-phase locking in a sharded or partitioned system (a per-shard mutex held briefly) or a fully optimistic compare-and-swap with retries, because holding a row lock across a service call is exactly the anti-pattern from 6.5.7. Postgres `FOR UPDATE` is fine within one database because it is local and cheap; across services it becomes a distributed lock problem, which is where you want idempotency keys and uniqueness constraints instead.

**MySQL's `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE`** behaves similarly to Postgres for the locking read, but under `REPEATABLE READ` it also takes gap/next-key locks, so `FOR UPDATE` on a non-unique predicate can lock a *range*, not just rows. A query like `SELECT ... WHERE status = 'pending' FOR UPDATE` locks every gap in the status index, which can block unrelated inserts of `status = 'pending'`. MySQL 8 supports `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` and `NOWAIT` too (`NOWAIT` arrived in 8.0; older versions treat `NOWAIT`/`SKIP LOCKED` syntax as an error rather than silently ignoring it).

## 6.6.5 A Concrete Deadlock, and How Postgres Detects It

```sql
-- transfer(id_from, id_to, amount)
BEGIN;                                    -- T1
UPDATE account SET balance = balance - 100 WHERE account_id = 1;   -- locks row 1
-- (application does a bit of work)
UPDATE account SET balance = balance + 100 WHERE account_id = 2;   -- wants row 2
```

```
T1                                T2
BEGIN;                            BEGIN;
UPDATE ... WHERE id=1  (lock 1)   UPDATE ... WHERE id=2  (lock 2)
UPDATE ... WHERE id=2  (needs 2)  UPDATE ... WHERE id=1  (needs 1)
        \                             /
         \-- waiting on each other ----/
```
```
ERROR:  deadlock detected
DETAIL:  Process 101 waits for ShareLock on transaction 202; blocked by process 100.
         Process 100 waits for ShareLock on transaction 201; blocked by process 101.
HINT:  See server log for query details.
SQLSTATE: 40P01
```

**How detection works.** Postgres waits `deadlock_timeout` (default 1 second) before declaring a wait "interesting". If, during that wait, the wait-for graph forms a cycle, it aborts one transaction immediately with `40P01`. Because detection happens after 1 second, deadlocks cost at least a second each, which is why you fix them instead of relying on detection. MySQL/InnoDB detects deadlocks immediately (it does not wait for `innodb_deadlock_detect`-style timeouts), so the same scenario errors instantly there.

**Prevention strategies, best first**
1. **Always acquire locks in a deterministic order** — usually primary key order.
```sql
BEGIN;
SELECT account_id, balance FROM account
WHERE account_id IN ($1, $2)
ORDER BY account_id          -- ← the fix
FOR UPDATE;
-- now apply both updates; no deadlock is possible because everyone queues in the same order
UPDATE account SET balance = balance - 100 WHERE account_id = $1;
UPDATE account SET balance = balance + 100 WHERE account_id = $2;
COMMIT;
```
2. **Keep transactions short** and never hold a lock while doing I/O.
3. **One row per transaction** when you can (an order line, not the whole order).
4. **`lock_timeout`** so a blocked transaction fails fast instead of tying up a pool connection:
```sql
SET LOCAL lock_timeout = '3s';
```
5. **Retry on `40P01`** in the application, same as `40001`.
6. **Advisory locks in a canonical order** for resources that are not rows.
7. Avoid `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` over a wide range; it multiplies the lock count and the deadlock surface.

## 6.6.6 Monitoring Locks

```sql
-- who is doing what right now
SELECT pid, usename, application_name, state,
       now() - xact_start  AS xact_age,
       now() - query_start AS query_age,
       wait_event_type, wait_event,
       left(query, 120) AS query
FROM   pg_stat_activity
WHERE  state <> 'idle'
ORDER  BY xact_start NULLS LAST;
```

```sql
-- the classic "who is blocking whom" query (PostgreSQL 9.6+ has ungranted pg_locks)
SELECT a.pid          AS blocked_pid,
       a.query        AS blocked_query,
       now() - a.query_start AS blocked_for,
       b.pid          AS blocking_pid,
       b.query        AS blocking_query
FROM   pg_stat_activity a
JOIN   pg_locks la ON la.pid = a.pid AND NOT la.granted
JOIN   pg_stat_activity b ON b.pid = la.pid
ORDER  BY a.query_start;
```
More robustly, use `pg_blocking_pids(a.pid)` (9.6+):
```sql
SELECT a.pid, pg_blocking_pids(a.pid) AS blockers,
       now() - a.query_start AS age, left(a.query,100)
FROM   pg_stat_activity a
WHERE  cardinality(pg_blocking_pids(a.pid)) > 0
ORDER  BY age DESC;
```

```sql
-- full lock view
SELECT l.locktype, l.relation::regclass, l.mode, l.granted, l.pid,
       l.transactionid, l.virtualxid
FROM   pg_locks l ORDER BY l.granted, l.pid;

-- idle-in-transaction sessions (the vacuum hazard from 6.5.6)
SELECT pid, now() - xact_start AS age, state, left(query,80)
FROM   pg_stat_activity
WHERE  state LIKE 'idle in transaction%'
ORDER  BY xact_start;

-- deadlock counter since the last stats reset
SELECT datname, deadlocks, xact_commit, xact_rollback,
       blks_read, blks_hit, temp_files
FROM   pg_stat_database WHERE datname = current_database();
```

Metrics worth alerting on: `pg_stat_database.deadlocks` (any sustained increase), `pg_stat_activity` count in `idle in transaction`, max `xact_age`, wait_event distribution from `pg_stat_activity` (`wait_event_type = 'Lock'`), `pg_stat_bgwriter.buffers_alloc` vs `buffers_hit` for cache pressure, and the `pg_locks` count per table for lock bloat.

InnoDB equivalents: `SHOW ENGINE INNODB STATUS\G` (the `LATEST DETECTED DEADLOCK` section), `performance_schema.data_lock_waits` and `data_locks` (8.0), `information_schema.innodb_trx`, and `SHOW FULL PROCESSLIST`.

**Model answer, Q11 (deadlock in payments):** "Context: a double-entry transfer service deadlocks roughly once per 40,000 transfers, each costing about 1 second before Postgres aborts one side. Decision: acquire both account row locks in primary key order with a single `SELECT ... WHERE account_id IN ($1,$2) ORDER BY account_id FOR UPDATE`, then apply the two updates, and set `lock_timeout` to three seconds. Reasoning: a deadlock is not a random event; it is a cycle in the wait-for graph, and the only structural fix is a global lock ordering so that two transactions can never hold what the other wants. Ordering by primary key gives every transaction the same sequence, so the second one simply queues. The current code locks the from-account and the to-account in whatever order the request payload happened to be in, which makes the cycle possible. Trade-offs: ordering by key means a transfer from account 2 to account 1 waits for account 1 first, which is fine, but if a batch touches many accounts, the ordering becomes the contention point and a batch lock with a short timeout is better. Failure scenario: an internal retry handled the deadlock silently for months, so latency spiked whenever two transfers crossed, and because the application retried without a cap, a hot deadlock produced a retry storm that saturated the pool. Prevention: retry with jitter and a bound, alert on the deadlock counter, keep transactions short, and consider `SERIALIZABLE` for the invariant if there are cross-account constraints; the retry is the safety net, not the design."

**Questions**
1. What are the four row lock modes, and which conflict?
2. Does Postgres escalate row locks to a table lock? (No.)
3. When are row locks released?
4. `NOWAIT` vs `SKIP LOCKED` vs `lock_timeout`: what does each do?
5. Give the two-transaction deadlock scenario and the exact error.
6. How does Postgres detect a deadlock, and how long does it wait first?
7. What is the canonical fix for lock-order deadlocks?
8. Session vs transaction advisory locks, and the PgBouncer trap.
9. Why is `pg_advisory_xact_lock` safe with transaction pooling and `pg_advisory_lock` not?
10. Optimistic vs pessimistic: name the failure mode of each if you get it wrong.
11. How do you implement a version-column compare-and-swap, and what do you do when it affects 0 rows?
12. Why does a 0-row `UPDATE` in the optimistic pattern need to be checked?
13. Which queries does `pg_locks` show you that `pg_stat_activity` cannot?
14. Write the "who is blocking whom" query.
15. What is `wait_event_type` and how does it differ from `state`?
16. Why can lock waits hurt even when the blocked query is `SELECT`?
17. When would you use `NOWAIT` instead of `lock_timeout`?
18. What does InnoDB do that Postgres does not, causing range `FOR UPDATE` to block inserts?

**Mistakes:** assuming lock waits show up as `state = 'active'` with no wait event; using session advisory locks behind a transaction pool; treating a deadlock as rare; retrying deadlocks without a cap; using `FOR UPDATE` with `GROUP BY`; assuming a `SELECT` cannot block.

**Mini task:** write a small script that spawns 8 workers doing random two-account transfers and reproduces the deadlock; then apply the `ORDER BY account_id FOR UPDATE` fix and run 100,000 transfers, recording the deadlock count before and after. Add `pg_blocking_pids` sampling so you can see the cycle live.

---

# 6.7 Indexes and Query Plans

Advanced · **Must-know** (this is where "senior" is usually decided)

## 6.7.1 B-Tree Internals

A Postgres B-tree index is a balanced tree of **pages** (8 KB by default, a compile-time constant). Rows live in **leaf tuples**; internal pages hold pointers down. Each leaf page holds a sorted run of index entries, each entry being the index key plus a pointer to the heap tuple — in Postgres that pointer is the `ctid` (page number, byte offset).

```
        root page (internal)          items: (key, left-child page)
        ┌───────────────────────┐
        │ (Ada,   p3)           │
        │ (Hal,   p7)           │
        └───────┬───────────────┘
                │
   ┌────────────┼─────────────────┐
   ▼            ▼                 ▼
 p3 (internal) p7 (internal)     p11 (leaf, linked)
   ├─ p1        ├─ p8             ├─ (Jan, (0,24))
   └─ p2        └─ p9             ├─ (Leo, (3,18))
                                  └─ (Zed, (9,02))
                                  ↔ (leaf pages are doubly linked for range scans)
```

Key consequences of this layout:
- **Height is logarithmic.** With ~8 KB pages, a small (narrow key, small tuple) leaf holds on the order of 200-500 entries, so a 3-level tree covers ~10^7-10^8 entries; a 4th level takes it to ~10^12. That is why "3 index levels" is normal at any realistic scale and why tree depth is not usually the problem.
- **A leaf search is a sequence of binary searches within a page**, plus one or more **buffered reads** (usually from `shared_buffers`, occasionally from the OS page cache or the device). The cost that matters is *I/O*, not CPU.
- **Sequential I/O vs random I/O.** A range scan over leaf pages follows the linked list, so the OS read-ahead and the storage stack turn it into large sequential reads — near the peak throughput of the device. A **unique index lookup by a random UUID** lands on a leaf chosen by chance, so it is a random read of 8 KB (or a 4 KB or 16 KB block on NVMe) for a few hundred bytes of useful data. At 100M entries the leaf level alone is tens of thousands of pages, which exceeds `shared_buffers`, so those reads go to disk.
- **Page splits.** An insert that belongs in the middle of a leaf splits the page, moving half the entries to a new page and pushing an entry up to the parent (which may split too). 50/50 splits are fast. A split that cannot proceed because there is no free space on the page triggers a "read error" path where the engine must wait for space; this is why an index on a monotonically increasing key is cheap (all inserts hit the rightmost leaf, which has room) and a random key is expensive (constant middle-of-page splits).
- **`fillfactor` and leaf density.** Default 90 for B-trees. Sequential inserts pack leaves near the right edge, so pages end up close to full; random inserts end up splitting mid-page and settling around 50-70% full. Lowering `fillfactor` on a heavily-updated index (e.g. 70) reserves space for HOT-style updates and can halve the page-split cost on update-heavy indexes, at the price of a permanently larger index.
- **HOT updates.** If an update changes no indexed column and there is free space on the heap page, Postgres writes the new tuple on the *same* page and adds no new index entry ("heap-only tuple"), so the index is untouched. Any update to an indexed column forces a new index entry for the new version, which is why wide, frequently-updated indexes are expensive.
- **Why not a hash index for ranges.** A hash index maps `hash(key) → bucket`. Lookups for equality are O(1) and hashes do not preserve order, so the engine cannot answer `WHERE created_at BETWEEN $1 AND $2`, `ORDER BY`, `MIN`/`MAX` over a range, or `ORDER BY ... LIMIT` on a non-key column. Hash indexes also cannot support multi-column keys and cannot do "nearest neighbour". They are only useful for equality-only, high-cardinality, large columns. MySQL's InnoDB has adaptive hash indexes; Postgres removed them in 2010 and does not recommend them.

## 6.7.2 Index Type Comparison

| Index type | Best for | Operators | Write cost | Size | Trade-offs |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **B-tree** (default) | Equality, ranges, prefix `LIKE 'abc%'`, `ORDER BY`, `min`/`max`, multi-column keys | `=`, `<`, `>`, `BETWEEN`, `IN`, `IS NULL`, `LIKE 'x%'`, `ORDER BY` | Logarithmic, page splits on random keys | Medium | Not good for full-text, arrays, containment, geometry, very low-selectivity booleans |
| **Hash** | Equality only, on wide/high-cardinality columns | `=` only | Cheap | Medium | No ranges, no ordering, single column only. Rarely the right answer |
| **GIN** (generalized inverted index) | Arrays, JSONB, full-text, trigram, ranges | `@>`, `<@`, `&&`, `?`, `?|`, `?&`, `@@`, `%`, `ILIKE` | Slow: many key entries per row; large WAL | Large | Fast lookups via posting lists, but no ordering, so it must combine with a bitmap heap scan or a B-tree; GIN "fastupdate" builds a pending list that must be flushed by `gin_clean_pending_list()` |
| **GiST** (generalized search tree) | Geometric, range types, nearest neighbour, exclusion constraints, full-text (alternative) | `&&`, `@>`, `<->`, `&&&`, ranges | Moderate | Large | Extensible with operator classes; lossless only for some types; exclusion constraints need it |
| **SP-GiST** | Highly non-uniform data: quadtrees, text prefixes | point-in-quad, prefix | Fast | Medium | Narrower use cases than GiST |
| **BRIN** (block range index) | Very large tables whose column is physically correlated with heap order (append-only timestamps) | Range predicates only | Tiny: a summary per 128-1280 pages | Microscopic | Requires correlation; useless if rows are updated in random heap positions. This is the index for a 2-billion-row append-only `events(created_at)` table |
| **Partial** (`WHERE ...`) | A stable subset: `WHERE deleted_at IS NULL`, hot tenants, recent rows | Whatever the base type supports | Less than a full index | Much smaller | **A query that does not imply the partial predicate cannot use it.** Adding a `WHERE status = 'active'` to the query is mandatory, and the predicate must be provably implied by the planner |
| **Expression** (`ON lower(email)`) | Case-insensitive or computed lookups | Comparison on the expression | Like a normal index | Medium | Must write the query with the same expression: `WHERE lower(email) = lower($1)`. Planner cannot always prove `email = $1` implies `lower(email) = lower($1)` |
| **Covering / `INCLUDE`** | Avoid the heap entirely for a narrow query | Same as base keys; any attribute for `INCLUDE` (not usable for search predicates) | Slightly larger writes | Larger | Enables index-only scans; only pays off when the extra width does not push the index out of cache |
| **Unique** | Enforce and accelerate uniqueness | Same as B-tree | Same, plus the constraint | Medium | Also used by the planner to eliminate duplicate-eliminating joins |
| **Multicolumn** | Composite predicates, covering specific queries | Leftmost prefix rules (below) | Same as B-tree | Wider | Order of columns is a schema decision; a badly ordered composite index is often unused entirely |

Postgres also has `UNIQUE NULLS NOT DISTINCT` (PG 15+), which makes NULLs collide in a unique index — necessary for "at most one NULL placeholder row per key".

**Leftmost-prefix rule.** For a composite index `(a, b, c)`, the index can serve a query that constrains a **prefix** of the columns:

| Query predicate | Uses `(a,b,c)`? | Best index |
|---|---|---|
| `a = 1` | Yes (a only) | `(a,b,c)` works |
| `a = 1 AND b = 2` | Yes | `(a,b,c)` |
| `a = 1 AND b = 2 AND c = 3` | Yes (full) | `(a,b,c)` |
| `b = 2` | **No** | `(b,...)` |
| `c = 3` | **No** | `(c,...)` |
| `a = 1 AND c = 3` | **Only `a`** | `(a,c)` |
| `b = 2 ORDER BY c` | No | `(b,c)` |

**The equality-then-range-then-sort rule.** Within a single index, columns are searched in order, and once you hit a range predicate (`>`, `<`, `BETWEEN`) the index can no longer use later columns to narrow the scan, though it can still use them for ordering *within* the range. So:

```sql
-- good: equality first, range second, sort satisfied by the index
CREATE INDEX ON orders (status, created_at DESC);
-- served by the index: WHERE status = 'paid' ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20

-- bad ordering: range first means the second column is useless for searching
CREATE INDEX ON orders (created_at, status);
-- WHERE status = 'paid' ORDER BY created_at DESC  -- cannot use index ordering
```

Multiple ranges can still be combined with a bitmap AND, so `(a, b)` can serve `a = 1 AND b > 5 AND b < 9` for *filtering*, just not for ordering.

## 6.7.3 Index-Only Scans and the Visibility Map

An **index-only scan** returns everything the index contains, without touching the heap. Requirements:
1. **All needed columns are in the index** (keys or `INCLUDE` payload).
2. **Every heap page the scan touches is marked all-visible in the visibility map**, which only `VACUUM` sets. Newly inserted or updated pages are not all-visible until vacuumed.
3. The plan must show `Heap Fetches: 0` to prove it worked.

```sql
CREATE INDEX orders_status_total_idx ON orders (status) INCLUDE (total, created_at);

EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, VERBOSE)
SELECT status, total, created_at FROM orders WHERE status = 'paid';
```
```
Index Only Scan using orders_status_total_idx on orders
  (cost=0.43..18422.10 rows=500000 width=40) (actual time=0.021..912.334 rows=500000 loops=1)
  Index Cond: (status = 'paid'::text)
  Heap Fetches: 0
  Buffers: shared hit=1204 read=3
```
`Heap Fetches: 0` is the certificate. If it is non-zero, either the pages are not all-visible (run `VACUUM (ANALYZE)`) or the planner overestimated. Large tables that are constantly updated rarely get all-visible pages, which is why index-only scans on hot tables sometimes degrade to real index scans with heap fetches.

## 6.7.4 How the Planner Chooses

Postgres' planner is **cost-based** and does exhaustive-ish search over join orders and access paths.

- **Cost units** are arbitrary but meaningful: `1.0` is one sequential page read from disk; a CPU cost of `0.01` is roughly one tuple comparison; `random_page_cost` (default 4.0, lower it to 1.1-2.0 on SSDs/NVMe) prices a random read; `effective_cache_size` (default 4 GB) tells it how much of the relation is likely cached. Both settings are `EXPLAIN`-visible only via the configuration and both massively change plans on real hardware.
- **Statistics** come from `ANALYZE`: per-column histograms (most common values, number of distinct values, frequency), correlation between physical and logical order, and `n_distinct` estimates. Stale statistics are the number-one cause of a "the query got slow overnight" incident.
- **Selectivity** estimates drive everything: `rows = input_rows × selectivity`. The planner uses equi-depth histograms, MCV lists, and expression statistics; if a column has never been analyzed the estimate defaults to a guess (often 0.5% of rows) which can be catastrophically wrong for skewed data.
- **Correlation** decides between index scan and bitmap heap scan on the same index: if a column is physically sorted with heap order (an append-only `created_at`), an index scan has good locality; if not, a bitmap heap scan (which reads the heap in physical order and tests many rows per page) wins.
- **Sequential-scan threshold** is not a number in Postgres. The planner compares `seq_page_cost × pages` against `random_page_cost × pages × selectivity` plus index overhead. The practical consequence: for a table with 1000 pages and an index that will fetch 300 rows, the planner will choose a seq scan, correctly, because reading the 1000-page table sequentially is cheaper than 300 random page reads. This is why "add an index" often does nothing on small tables, and why `random_page_cost` matters on real SSDs.
- **Partial plan shapes**: `Seq Scan`, `Index Scan`, `Index Only Scan`, `Bitmap Heap Scan` (two-phase: build a bitmap of matching pages from the index, then read the heap in physical order), `Tid Scan`, plus joins (`Nested Loop`, `Hash Join`, `Merge Join`), aggregates (`HashAggregate`, `GroupAggregate`, `WindowAgg`), sorting (`Sort`, `Incremental Sort`), and `Gather`/`Gather Merge` for parallel query.
- **Parallel query**: the planner sets `max_parallel_workers_per_gather` based on table size and cost, splits the scan, and uses a `Gather` node. Parallelism does not apply to a query that the planner thinks is cheap, so a 5-second query that never goes parallel is a signal that its estimates are wrong.

## 6.7.5 Reading a Real Plan, Node by Node

Fixture: `orders` with 2,000,000 rows, `status` with 3 distinct values where `'pending'` is 3% of rows, and this query:

```sql
SELECT * FROM orders WHERE status = 'pending' ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20;
```

**The bad plan (no composite index)**
```sql
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, VERBOSE)
SELECT * FROM orders WHERE status = 'pending' ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20;
```
```
Limit  (cost=2947.55..2947.56 rows=20 width=63) (actual time=842.331..842.340 rows=20 loops=1)
  Buffers: shared hit=90
  ->  Sort  (cost=2911.05..2947.56 rows=59600 width=63)
        (actual time=842.329..842.334 rows=20 loops=1)
        Sort Key: orders.created_at DESC
        Sort Method: top-N  Memory: 33kB
        ->  Seq Scan on orders  (cost=0.00..7541.00 rows=59600 width=63)
              (actual time=0.014..655.117 rows=59608 loops=1)
              Filter: (status = 'pending'::text)
              Rows Removed by Filter: 1940392
              Buffers: shared hit=90 read=11834 dirtied=214
              Storage: Memory 29kB
Planning Time: 0.118 ms
Execution Time: 842.402 ms
```
Read it outside-in:
1. **`Limit` (top, actual 842ms)** — the node the caller cares about.
2. **`Sort`, Method: top-N, Memory: 33kB** — Postgres is smarter than "sort everything": because there is a `LIMIT 20` above it, it keeps only 20 rows in memory. So the sort is *not* the bottleneck; the 842 ms is spent in the child.
3. **`Seq Scan`, actual 655 ms, Rows Removed by Filter: 1,940,392** — it read all 2,000,000 rows (10,924 pages) to keep 59,608, then the Sort + Limit threw away 59,588 of them. Buffers: `hit=90 read=11834` means 11,834 pages came from the OS/disk and only 90 were already in shared buffers.
4. **The bug**: the `Limit` cannot push down, so the engine materialized the whole matching set. `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` without the plan change would keep doing 655 ms of work to return 20 rows.

**The fix**
```sql
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY orders_status_created_at_idx ON orders (status, created_at DESC);
```
```
Limit  (cost=0.43..1.86 rows=20 width=63) (actual time=0.048..0.052 rows=20 loops=1)
  ->  Index Scan using orders_status_created_at_idx on orders
        (cost=0.43..1.79 rows=20 width=63) (actual time=0.044..0.049 rows=20 loops=1)
        Index Cond: (status = 'pending'::text)
        Buffers: shared hit=5
Planning Time: 3.914 ms
Execution Time: 0.081 ms
```
Ten thousand times faster, and now the plan matches the query's intent: `Index Cond` on `status`, ordered output for free, `Limit` stops after 20 entries. No `Sort` node at all, because the index already provides the order.

**A nested loop join with buffers**
```sql
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT c.name, o.total
FROM   customers c JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.customer_id
WHERE  c.country = 'IN' AND o.status = 'shipped';
```
```
Nested Loop  (cost=1.15..98543.22 rows=8421 width=52)
  Buffers: shared hit=41203 read=3011
  ->  Index Scan using customers_country_idx on customers c
        (cost=0.42..1560.00 rows=8421 width=19)
        (actual time=0.011..8.204 rows=8421 loops=1)
        Index Cond: (country = 'IN'::text)
        Buffers: shared hit=61
  ->  Index Scan using orders_customer_status_idx on orders o
        (cost=0.43..11.31 rows=11 width=33)
        (actual time=0.004..10.883 rows=11.02 loops=8421)
        Index Cond: (o.customer_id = c.customer_id)
        Buffers: shared hit=41142 read=2950
Planning Time: 0.345 ms
Execution Time: 9821.104 ms
```
Reading: the outer loop scanned 8,421 customers via an index (`actual rows=8421` vs estimated 8421 — good estimate). The inner loop is a **Nested Loop**, the right choice because the outer side is small and the inner side has an index; note `loops=8421` on the inner node and its `actual rows=11.02` per loop — multiply by the loops for the total (~92,830 rows). The `Buffers: shared hit=41142 read=2950` line is where the time went. If the estimates had been off, the planner might have chosen a Hash Join, and `loops=1` would tell you so.

**A bitmap heap scan**
```
Bitmap Heap Scan on orders  (cost=935.42..2104.15 rows=59600 width=40)
  Recheck Cond: (status = 'pending'::text)
  Heap Blocks: exact=1295 lossy=120  (cost range... )
  ->  Bitmap Index Scan on orders_status_idx
        (cost=112.36..112.36 rows=59600 width=0)
        Index Cond: (status = 'pending'::text)
```
`exact=1295 lossy=120` means 120 blocks could not fit in `work_mem` and their exact row offsets were discarded, so the heap must recheck every row in those blocks with the `Recheck Cond`. If `lossy` approaches `exact`, raise `work_mem`.

**Red flags to look for**
| Signal | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Estimated rows vastly different from `actual rows` (10x+) | Stale or insufficient statistics; run `ANALYZE`, or add extended statistics |
| `Rows Removed by Filter` near the table size | The filter is not selective, so the scan is a seq scan wearing a hat |
| `Buffers: shared read` >> `hit` on a hot table | Cache miss / IO bound |
| `temp_read` / `temp_blocks` written | A sort or hash spilled to disk; raise `work_mem` or fix the query |
| `Heap Fetches: 0` on an index-only scan | Success. Non-zero means the visibility map is not helping |
| `Nested Loop` with `actual rows` >> 1 in the inner side and no index | Join explosion or a missing index on the join key |
| `HashAggregate` + huge `Batches` > 1 | Aggregate spilled to disk |
| `Seq Scan` on a small table | Usually correct, not a problem |
| `Lossy` blocks near `exact` | `work_mem` too small |
| `Gather Merge` / `Workers Launched: 0` on a long query | Parallelism not engaged, estimates wrong |
| `Planning Time` >> `Execution Time` | Prepared statement with a bad generic plan, or too many partitions (see 6.8) |

**Extended statistics** for correlated columns:
```sql
CREATE STATISTICS orders_status_created_stat (dependencies, ndistinct)
  ON status, created_at FROM orders;
ANALYZE orders;
```

## 6.7.6 The Complete List of Reasons an Index Is Ignored

| # | Cause | Example | Fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Function or expression on the column** | `WHERE date(created_at) = '2024-01-01'` with an index only on `created_at` | Index the expression: `CREATE INDEX ON orders ((date(created_at)))`; or rewrite to a range (`created_at >= '2024-01-01' AND created_at < '2024-01-02'`) |
| 2 | **Implicit type cast** | `WHERE user_id = '42'` where `user_id` is `bigint`: the literal is untyped and the planner wraps the column in a cast, so the plain index is unusable | Compare like-to-like: `user_id = 42` or `user_id = $1::bigint`. Same class: `WHERE ts::date = ...`, `WHERE name = 'x' COLLATE "C"` |
| 3 | **Leading wildcard** | `WHERE name LIKE '%son'` | Use `pg_trgm` (GIN) for fuzzy search, or a full-text index; or anchor the pattern `LIKE 'son%'` |
| 4 | **`OR` across different columns** | `WHERE email = $1 OR phone = $2` | `UNION ALL` of two indexed lookups, or a BitmapOr which works if *each* branch has an index |
| 5 | **Table too small** | 400-row table | Nothing. A seq scan is cheaper and the planner is right |
| 6 | **Low selectivity** | `WHERE is_active = true` at 90% of rows; `WHERE gender = 'f'` | Partial index on the rare case (`WHERE NOT deleted`), or accept the seq scan |
| 7 | **Stale statistics / correlation** | Plan flipped after autovacuum | `ANALYZE`, tune `autovacuum_analyze_*`, create extended statistics |
| 8 | **Sort-order mismatch** | Index is `(a, b)` and the query is `ORDER BY a DESC, b DESC` | Postgres can scan a B-tree backwards, so a full reversal is fine; a *mixed* order (`a ASC, b DESC`) is not — index on `(a, b DESC)` or sort explicitly |
| 9 | **NULL-heavy column** | `WHERE deleted_at IS NULL` with 99% NULLs: the B-tree contains 500M NULL entries but they are not selective | Partial index `ON orders (id) WHERE deleted_at IS NULL` |
| 10 | **Collation mismatch** | Index built under `LC_COLLATE=C`, query runs under a different collation, or `lower()`/`upper()` mismatch the index expression | Use `citext`, an expression index on `lower(col)`, or a `COLLATE`-qualified index. Also: case-insensitive equality (`email = 'A@x.com'` matching `'a@x.com'`) needs `citext` or `lower()` on both sides |
| 11 | **Leading `OR` with a non-indexable branch**, or `NOT`, `!=`, `IS DISTINCT FROM`, `LIKE '%x'` mixed into the predicate | `WHERE deleted_at IS NULL OR deleted_at > now()` | Rewrite as a union of two indexable branches, or a partial index |
| 12 | **Small LIMIT with an unsatisfied sort** | `ORDER BY popularity DESC LIMIT 10` — the planner correctly prefers a top-N heapsort over an index scan | Sometimes this is right; sometimes you need an index on `(popularity DESC)` |
| 13 | **Type mismatch in a parameter** | A prepared statement where the driver sends the parameter as `unknown` and the planner picks a generic plan | Cast the parameter in SQL: `$1::bigint` |
| 14 | **The query is inside a function** (`plpgsql`/`plpython`) | SQL inside plpgsql is planned once, per session, and often without statistics | Move the query out of the function, or use a dynamic `EXECUTE` with format arguments |
| 15 | **Partition pruning leaves nothing**, or the partition list is too long to plan | Query spanning 5,000 partitions | Fewer partitions, or route by a coarse key |
| 16 | **The index is invalid** | After a failed `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` | `SELECT * FROM pg_index WHERE NOT indisvalid;` then `REINDEX` |

Confirm with `EXPLAIN (VERBOSE)` and look for a `Seq Scan` where you expected an index, then check the *estimated* rows against reality before assuming the planner is wrong: a wrong plan is far more often wrong statistics than a wrong cost model.

## 6.7.7 Index Cost, Bloat, and REINDEX

**The cost of too many indexes.** Every index is a B-tree that must be updated on every insert, every delete (a tombstone entry), and every update of an indexed column. On a write-heavy table with 10 indexes:

- Each `INSERT` does 10 index inserts; each is a random write into a different structure, so the write path stops being one sequential WAL append and becomes a scatter across 10 trees.
- Each `UPDATE` of a non-key column still dirties up to 10 trees unless it can be HOT; the moment one indexed column changes, the update is not HOT and every index gets a new entry.
- The buffer cache now has 10 trees competing for `shared_buffers`, so the working set per query falls and hit ratio drops for all queries.
- WAL volume roughly triples, which lengthens checkpoints and increases replication lag.
- Backup size and restore time grow linearly.
- `VACUUM` has more index entries to clean, so it runs longer and holds more locks.

Rule of thumb: index the columns your queries actually filter and sort on, drop duplicates (`pg_stat_user_indexes.idx_scan = 0` is a strong signal, though not proof: an index used once a month for a report still earns its place), and check the write path explicitly before adding an index to a hot table.

**Bloat.** Every `UPDATE` and `DELETE` leaves a dead tuple. In the heap, `VACUUM` marks them dead for reuse but the file does not shrink; only `VACUUM FULL` (which writes a new, compact file under an `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` lock) or `pg_repack` (online, with a short lock at the end) returns space to the OS. In indexes, dead entries can only be removed by a `REINDEX` (also a full rebuild, `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` unless `REINDEX CONCURRENTLY`) — a B-tree cannot defragment in place. Bloat symptoms: the table file grows while the row count stays flat, index-only scans lose their `Heap Fetches: 0`, scans read many more pages than the tuple count implies, and `pg_stat_user_tables.n_dead_tup` is high.

```sql
-- bloat estimation (a standard "official-ish" catalog query)
SELECT relname,
       n_live_tup, n_dead_tup,
       round(100.0 * n_dead_tup / NULLIF(n_live_tup + n_dead_tup, 0), 2) AS dead_pct,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_total_relation_size(relid)) AS total_size,
       last_autovacuum, last_autoanalyze
FROM   pg_stat_user_tables
WHERE  n_dead_tup > 10000
ORDER  BY n_dead_tup DESC;

-- index usage: candidates for dropping
SELECT relname, indexrelname, idx_scan, idx_tup_read, pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(indexrelid))
FROM   pg_stat_user_indexes ORDER BY idx_scan;

-- index size per table
SELECT relname, indexrelname, pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(indexrelid)) AS idx_size
FROM   pg_stat_user_indexes WHERE relname = 'orders' ORDER BY pg_relation_size(indexrelid) DESC;

-- live tuples vs page count
SELECT relname, relpages, reltuples FROM pg_class WHERE relname = 'orders';
```

**REINDEX:**
```sql
REINDEX INDEX CONCURRENTLY orders_status_idx;   -- no ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock, slower, cannot run in a tx
REINDEX TABLE orders;                           -- ACCESS EXCLUSIVE on each index
REINDEX INDEX orders USING btree;               -- change index method
REINDEX (VERBOSE) TABLE orders;
```

## 6.7.8 The Optimization Workflow

1. **Measure first.** `EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, VERBOSE)` on the real query with real parameters. A query in a loop with different parameters can have a different plan each time; log the plans (`auto_explain` with `log_analyze`, `log_min_duration_statement`), not just the slow-query list.
2. **Find the dominant node.** Compare `actual time` between parent and child to find where the time is spent; compare `actual rows` with `rows` to find where the estimates diverge. A node whose actual time is not much larger than its child's is not your problem.
3. **Classify the fix:**
   - Missing index for the predicate → add a partial/expression/covering index.
   - Bad plan shape (seq scan on a big selective table, wrong join method) → usually a statistics or parameter problem first, index second.
   - Sort/hash spilling → `work_mem` (carefully, per statement) or rewrite.
   - Join explosion → fix the query shape (semi-join, pre-aggregate), not the index.
   - Too many rows returned → `LIMIT`, a better predicate, or pagination by keyset instead of offset.
4. **Verify with `EXPLAIN (ANALYZE)` again** and confirm buffers dropped.
5. **Check the write cost** you just added.
6. **Prevent regressions.** Add the query to a plan-regression test: run `EXPLAIN` and assert the plan shape (e.g. "must contain `Index Scan`", "must not contain `Seq Scan` on `orders`"), or at least assert latency against a fixture. Pin `enable_seqscan = off` in a test to detect when an index stops being used.
7. **Change data, not just plans.** Often the real fix is a different data model: a denormalized read table, a materialized view, a pre-aggregated summary, or partitioning.

**Keyset pagination** as a durable optimization:
```sql
-- slow, and gets slower with page number
SELECT * FROM orders WHERE status = 'pending' ORDER BY created_at DESC, order_id DESC LIMIT 50 OFFSET 100000;
-- fast, constant cost
SELECT * FROM orders
WHERE status = 'pending'
  AND (created_at, order_id) < ($1_ts, $1_id)
ORDER BY created_at DESC, order_id DESC
LIMIT 50;
```
Requires a stable, unique, indexed sort key, which is exactly the determinism rule from 6.4.

**Model answer, Q4 (plan flipped):** "Context: a dashboard query that had run in 40 ms for months suddenly takes 12 seconds, and the only change near the deployment was that autovacuum had run on a table whose `status` distribution is heavily skewed. Investigation: `EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)` showed the plan flipped from an index scan to a seq scan, with an estimate of 3 rows against 400,000 actual rows. Decision: `ANALYZE orders` immediately to restore service, then fix the root cause. Reasoning: nothing about the query changed; the planner's belief about the data did. `status` is highly skewed — 97% `shipped`, 3% `pending` — and the planner was using a default selectivity because it no longer had an up-to-date histogram for the correlation it needed. Trade-offs: re-analyzing only fixes the estimate, not the structural problem; if this column is central to the query, I want a plan that does not depend on a stale histogram. Failure scenario: we re-analyze every morning, the same flip happens after each bulk load, and the first user request after each load pays the seq scan. Prevention: set a lower `autovacuum_analyze_scale_factor` and `autovacuum_analyze_threshold` for high-churn tables, analyze explicitly at the end of a bulk load, and consider a partial index for the hot subset, which removes the dependence on statistics entirely because it is only ever scanned when the predicate implies its own condition."

**Questions**
1. Explain B-tree page layout, fanout, and why the depth stays small.
2. Why do random UUID keys cause page splits and what is the fill-factor effect?
3. What does a B-tree buy that a hash index cannot do, and vice versa?
4. Leftmost-prefix rule: which of these queries use `(a,b,c)`?
5. Why must equality columns come before range columns in a composite index?
6. What are the three requirements of an index-only scan, and how do you verify it?
7. What is the visibility map and who sets it?
8. What inputs does the Postgres cost model use, and what do `random_page_cost` and `effective_cache_size` change?
9. There is no `sequential_scan_threshold` in Postgres. What decides seq scan vs index scan?
10. How do histogram-based selectivity and correlation differ in what they affect?
11. Read a plan node by node: what do `loops`, `rows` vs `actual rows`, `Buffers: shared hit/read`, and `Rows Removed by Filter` tell you?
12. What is a `top-N` sort, and why did it not help the bad plan?
13. When is a `Bitmap Heap Scan` better than an `Index Scan`?
14. What do `exact=` and `lossy=` in `Heap Blocks` mean?
15. List at least ten reasons an index might be ignored, and how to confirm each.
16. What is the cost of an extra index on a write-heavy table?
17. What is index bloat, how do you measure it, and what fixes it without downtime?
18. `REINDEX` vs `REINDEX CONCURRENTLY` vs `pg_repack`: which locks what?
19. Why does an update to an indexed column block HOT updates?
20. `fillfactor` on an update-heavy index: when does it help?
21. Keyset vs offset pagination: why is one constant-cost?
22. What are extended statistics and what problem do they solve?
23. Parallel query: when does the planner engage it, and what node shows it?
24. How do you make sure an optimization does not regress?

**Mistakes:** adding an index without measuring; blaming the planner instead of the statistics; using `WHERE date(col) = ...`; `WHERE int_col = '5'`; `LIKE '%x'`; an index on a 95%-selective column; `LIMIT` without `ORDER BY` and then blaming pagination; adding ten indexes to a write-heavy table; `REINDEX` during peak traffic.

**Mini task:** build a table with 5M rows and a `status` column skewed 97/3. Time the same query with (a) no index, (b) index on `status`, (c) index on `(status, created_at DESC)`, (d) index on `created_at` alone, and (e) an expression index. Then run `VACUUM`, change the distribution with a bulk `UPDATE`, and re-time without re-analyzing to reproduce a flipped plan; then `ANALYZE` and re-time. Capture `EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)` for each.

---

# PHASE 6, PART 1 (CONTINUED): SQL Deep Dive, Operations, and Caching

**Dialect convention.** PostgreSQL throughout unless the text says otherwise. Every place where MySQL 8, SQLite, SQL Server, or Oracle does something materially different is called out in 6.12. Where an example is dialect-specific, the SQL comment names the engine.

**Why this part exists.** Sections 6.1 to 6.7 gave you the model, the language, the isolation semantics, and the planner. This part covers everything *above* the query: the schema objects most teams use casually and therefore get wrong (views, triggers, procedural code), the runtime plumbing that turns a query into a service (connection pooling), the operational layer that turns a database into a system you can page someone about at 3am (replication, backups, partitions, migrations), and the caching layer that sits on top of all of it.

The through-line of this part: **every abstraction that hides the database eventually shows up as a latency budget, a lock, or a correctness bug.** Views hide plans. Triggers hide writes. Poolers hide connections. Caches hide staleness. The interview is not "do you know the syntax"; it is "can you say what the hidden thing costs".

---

# 6.8 Views, materialized views, and triggers

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition.**
- A **view** is a stored `SELECT` query, expanded by the planner at each reference. It owns no storage. It is a *naming and reuse* device with one side effect: it can restrict what a role can see, which makes it a permissions tool as much as a query tool.
- A **materialized view** is a stored query *plus* its results persisted as a real table, refreshed explicitly (`REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW`) or by trigger (`WITH NO DATA` + a trigger). It trades freshness for read latency.
- A **trigger** is a function invoked automatically by the database on `INSERT`/`UPDATE`/`DELETE`, either `FOR EACH ROW` or `FOR EACH STATEMENT`. It is the escape hatch for logic the declarative schema cannot express, and the primary source of "why is this write slow" and "why did that row change".

## 6.8.1 Views: mechanics

```sql
-- Plain view. CREATE OR REPLACE VIEW fails if the new column list differs
-- (you cannot drop or reorder columns, and cannot change the output type).
CREATE VIEW v_active_customers AS
SELECT c.id,
       c.name,
       c.email,
       o.last_order_at,
       o.lifetime_value
FROM customers c
JOIN LATERAL (
    SELECT max(created_at) AS last_order_at,
           sum(total_cents)  AS lifetime_value
    FROM orders o
    WHERE o.customer_id = c.id
) o ON true
WHERE c.deleted_at IS NULL;
```

**Expansion, not storage.** Postgres stores the parse tree. `EXPLAIN (VERBOSE, COSTS OFF) SELECT * FROM v_active_customers WHERE id = 42;` shows the underlying `customers` and `orders` scans, with the view definition spliced in. Consequences:

- **The view is re-planned every time.** A view referencing eight tables does not get a "compiled plan" you can inspect. This is why an ORM that generates one query per joined relation (a classic nested-select view model) is slow: it is not views, it is N+1 with extra steps.
- **Inlining can change semantics.** A view with a `LIMIT` inlined into an outer query with a `DISTINCT` can return fewer rows, because the limit is applied before dedup. Postgres avoids some of this by not inlining `WITH` / `OFFSET` / `LIMIT` / set-returning-function views in older versions, and modern versions do inline with "runoff" protection; MySQL has its own merge-vs-temporary-table decision. Know that the inlining rules are version-specific and that the *observable* behaviour is what you must test, not the rule.
- **Security barrier views.** A view is not a security boundary by default: a predicate on the view can be pushed below a user-supplied function call, letting that function see rows it should not. Fix:

```sql
CREATE VIEW v_own_invoices WITH (security_barrier = true) AS
SELECT * FROM invoices WHERE account_id = current_setting('app.account_id')::bigint;
```

- **Permissions via views.** Granting `SELECT` on `v_invoices` and revoking it on `invoices` gives the role a stable, narrow surface. This is the *good* pattern for multi-tenant isolation, and it composes with row-level security (6.6/Phase 6 migrations) rather than replacing it.
- **`information_schema.views` and `pg_views`** show definitions; `\d+ v` shows the columns. `pg_get_viewdef(oid)` returns the reconstructed SQL, which is what you want for a schema audit.
- **Updating through a simple view** works if the view is automatically updatable (single base table, no `DISTINCT`/`GROUP BY`/set ops/`LIMIT`). A join view is not automatically updatable, so `UPDATE v_active_customers SET ...` fails; you need an `INSTEAD OF` trigger, which is a sign you should have written the statement against the base table.
- **Recursive views** are written with `CREATE RECURSIVE VIEW`; the more common form is a recursive CTE inside a normal view.
- **Performance trap: views over views.** Ten stacked single-table views are still a flat plan, so they are not automatically slow. Ten stacked views *each with a join* are ten joins, and the planner's search space is real.

**When to use a view vs not.**

| Use a view | Do not use a view |
|---|---|
| Reuse a multi-table join across many call sites with a stable column contract | Wrap a single-table `SELECT *` — that is a table alias with extra steps |
| Enforce a permissions boundary (`security_barrier`, restricted grants) | To make a slow query "look modular"; the plan cost is unchanged |
| Freeze a semantic definition: "active order" means one thing everywhere | For parameters — views cannot take arguments; use a function, or `pg_temp` |
| Provide a backwards-compatible contract while you migrate the underlying tables (expand/contract) | As a schema for a reporting warehouse where you want performance too |

## 6.8.2 Materialized views: mechanics

```sql
CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW mv_daily_revenue
WITH (fillfactor = 80) AS
SELECT date_trunc('day', created_at) AS day,
       tenant_id,
       sum(total_cents) FILTER (WHERE status = 'paid') AS paid_cents,
       count(*)                                    AS order_count
FROM orders
WHERE created_at >= timestamptz '2024-01-01'
GROUP BY 1, 2;

-- Needs at least a UNIQUE index for CONCURRENTLY.
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX ON mv_daily_revenue (day, tenant_id);

REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW mv_daily_revenue;               -- takes ACCESS EXCLUSIVE
REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW CONCURRENTLY mv_daily_revenue;  -- takes SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE
```

**The refresh trade is precise:**

| Refresh mode | Lock | Readers see | Cost | Use when |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Plain `REFRESH` | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` on the view | Nothing, for the duration | Cheap; full rebuild | Offline, or the view has an internal dependency |
| `CONCURRENTLY` | `SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE` (does not block reads or writes) | Old snapshot until swap, then new | Roughly two rebuilds; runs a `UNIQUE` diff to find changed rows | The view is on the read path of a live system |

`CONCURRENTLY` has hard requirements: a `UNIQUE` index covering all rows, it cannot run inside a transaction block, it cannot refresh a view with column defaults added in PG 15+ (actually allowed since PG 15? — verify against your version: PG 12 added the `WITH NO DATA`/default-column interaction restrictions; treat "check your version's docs" as the correct interview answer), and a failed concurrent refresh leaves the view untouched.

**Incrementally refreshed views** (logical replication based, PG 15+ experimental; or maintained by hand) exist because a full rebuild of a 200M-row rollup every hour is wasteful. The common practical pattern is a **delta table plus a merge**:

```sql
-- Instead of a full refresh, apply only today's changes, then swap atomically.
BEGIN;
CREATE TEMP TABLE mv_daily_revenue_next (LIKE mv_daily_revenue INCLUDING ALL) ON COMMIT DROP;
INSERT INTO mv_daily_revenue_next
SELECT * FROM <the same rollup query, restricted to the changed window>;
DELETE FROM mv_daily_revenue_next WHERE day < current_date - 7;  -- retention
COMMIT;
-- swap in a single transaction so readers never see a partial rollup
```

**Production considerations for materialized views:**
- **Read-only to the application.** Grant `SELECT` only. A `REFRESH` that runs concurrently with a rogue `INSERT` corrupts your derived data and you will not notice for a week.
- **Unique index or the concurrent refresh fails.** This is the single most common `REFRESH CONCURRENTLY` error.
- **Refresh cadence vs staleness SLO.** State it: "the dashboard is stale by at most one refresh interval, 15 minutes, monitored by `pg_stat_user_matviews` and a custom `max(age)` check."
- **Cost is a full scan or a full index build of the underlying query.** If the source is 500M rows, an hourly full refresh is your biggest consumer of `shared_buffers` and WAL. Measure it; schedule it off-peak; consider incremental.
- **Dependency chains.** A matview depending on another matview makes refresh ordering a scheduling problem. `pg_depend` is your graph.

**When to use a materialized view vs not.** Use it for (a) aggregations over millions of rows that run on a read path, (b) denormalized read models where the staleness window is acceptable and stated, (c) expensive joins whose result changes rarely. Do not use it when the data must be read-your-writes (see 6.17.13), when the refresh cost approaches the query cost, or when you could simply add the right index — a `GROUP BY tenant_id, date_trunc('day', created_at)` with a matching index may make the materialized view unnecessary. Always check that first; it is the cheaper answer.

## 6.8.3 Triggers: mechanics

```sql
CREATE FUNCTION audit_order_changes() RETURNS trigger
LANGUAGE plpgsql
AS $$
BEGIN
    IF TG_OP = 'DELETE' THEN
        INSERT INTO audit_log(tbl, op, row_pk, old_row, at)
        VALUES (TG_TABLE_NAME, TG_OP, OLD.id, to_jsonb(OLD), now());
        RETURN OLD;   -- required; returning NULL silently skips the operation
    ELSIF TG_OP = 'INSERT' THEN
        INSERT INTO audit_log(tbl, op, row_pk, new_row, at)
        VALUES (TG_TABLE_NAME, TG_OP, NEW.id, to_jsonb(NEW), now());
        RETURN NEW;
    ELSE
        INSERT INTO audit_log(tbl, op, row_pk, old_row, new_row, at)
        VALUES (TG_TABLE_NAME, TG_OP, NEW.id, to_jsonb(OLD), to_jsonb(NEW), now());
        RETURN NEW;
    END IF;
END;
$$;

CREATE TRIGGER orders_audit
AFTER INSERT OR UPDATE OR DELETE ON orders
FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION audit_order_changes();
```

Mechanics worth knowing cold:
- **Timing**: `BEFORE` (can modify `NEW`, can return `NULL` to cancel the operation), `AFTER` (sees the final state, cannot cancel).
- **Granularity**: `FOR EACH ROW` fires per affected row; `FOR EACH STATEMENT` fires once. A statement-level trigger with a transition table (`REFERENCING NEW TABLE AS newtab`) gives you set-based access, which is the only performant way to audit a bulk update.
- **`WHEN (condition)`** filters rows before the function is called, which saves a function call per non-matching row.
- **Return value**: in a `BEFORE ROW` trigger you must return `NEW` or `OLD`. Returning `NULL` from a `BEFORE` trigger *silently skips* the row — a classic source of "my trigger sometimes drops rows".
- **Ordering**: triggers of the same kind fire in alphabetical name order in Postgres. Name them `01_...`, `02_...` if order matters.
- **`SECURITY DEFINER`** lets the trigger write as its owner. This is how you safely write to a table the application role cannot touch, and it is also a privilege-escalation surface: `SECURITY DEFINER` functions must have a locked-down `search_path` (`SET search_path = pg_catalog, public`).
- **`CONSTRAINT TRIGGER`** (`AFTER ... DEFERRABLE INITIALLY DEFERRED`) fires at commit time, which is the only way to enforce a cross-row invariant at transaction end (cycle detection in a graph, for example).
- **Row count = performance cost.** A row trigger on a 500k-row `UPDATE` runs 500k PL/pgSQL invocations. Every invocation is an interpreter context switch. This is the #1 cause of "the migration ran for 40 minutes and I do not know why".

```sql
-- Fast set-based audit: one statement, no per-row function call.
CREATE TRIGGER orders_audit_stmt
AFTER UPDATE ON orders
REFERENCING NEW TABLE AS newtab OLD TABLE AS oldtab
FOR EACH STATEMENT EXECUTE FUNCTION audit_bulk();

CREATE FUNCTION audit_bulk() RETURNS trigger LANGUAGE sql AS $$
  INSERT INTO audit_log(tbl, op, row_pk, new_row, at)
  SELECT TG_TABLE_NAME, 'UPDATE', n.id, to_jsonb(n), now() FROM newtab n;
$$;
```

**Where triggers are genuinely the right tool:**
- **Audit logging of `INSERT`/`UPDATE`/`DELETE` on sensitive tables**, where the requirement is "no application bug can bypass the log". Note the honest caveat: a trigger can be bypassed by a superuser, by `TRUNCATE` (triggers do not fire unless you declare `AFTER TRUNCATE`), and by disabling it in an emergency. If the audit must be legally defensible, ship the WAL.
- **Maintaining a denormalized counter or aggregate** in the same transaction, so the derived value is never stale. The counter pattern:

```sql
CREATE FUNCTION bump_post_comment_count() RETURNS trigger
LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
BEGIN
  IF TG_OP = 'INSERT' THEN
    UPDATE posts SET comment_count = comment_count + 1 WHERE id = NEW.post_id;
  ELSIF TG_OP = 'DELETE' THEN
    UPDATE posts SET comment_count = GREATEST(comment_count - 1, 0) WHERE id = OLD.post_id;
  END IF;
  RETURN NULL;
END $$;
CREATE TRIGGER comment_count_trg AFTER INSERT OR DELETE ON comments
FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION bump_post_comment_count();
```

- **Enforcing a cross-row invariant** that no `CHECK` can express (no cycles, no overlap — although 6.6 already showed you an exclusion constraint, which is better because it is declarative and race-free).
- **Deriving a value at write time** so no caller can forget it: `updated_at = now()`, a denormalized `search_vector`.

**Where triggers are the wrong tool (and this is the interesting part):**

| Anti-pattern | Why it hurts | Better |
|---|---|---|
| Trigger enforcing a check a `CHECK` constraint could express | The error surfaces at write time but the rule is invisible to readers, tools, and the next hire; no `\d` documentation | `CHECK` / `NOT NULL` / `UNIQUE` / FK |
| Trigger maintaining a counter *without* the row's own transaction owning the update | Every counter write is an extra row lock, on a *different* table, in the hot path: deadlock potential and contention | Application-side guarded `UPDATE ... SET c = c + 1` with `RETURNING`, or compute on read |
| Trigger that is "business logic" hidden from the application | Untestable without hitting the database, invisible to code review, and it runs on every writer including ETL and admin tools | Application service, or a stored procedure that is the only writer |
| Trigger for cache invalidation | Couples the database to a cache's keyspace; a hard failure inside an `AFTER` trigger aborts the write | Transactional outbox + consumer (6.17.13) |
| Trigger doing network I/O (`pg_net`, HTTP calls, Kafka from the DB) | A slow or hung network call inside a write path; unbounded latency; cascading failures | Outbox pattern |
| Multiple triggers cascading | Trigger A updates B, whose trigger updates C: lock order is implicit and deadlock-prone | Do it in one explicit procedure you control |

**Common mistakes**
1. Forgetting `RETURN NEW`/`RETURN OLD` in a PL/pgSQL trigger function. Postgres raises "control reached end of trigger procedure without RETURN" — noisy, so people work around it wrongly by returning early, which cancels rows.
2. Not using `SECURITY DEFINER SET search_path`, leaving an object-shadowing exploit open.
3. Forgetting that `TRUNCATE` fires only statement-level `AFTER TRUNCATE` triggers.
4. Recursive trigger loops: an `AFTER UPDATE` trigger on `orders` that updates `orders` re-fires. Guard with `WHEN` or a `pg_trigger_depth()` check.
5. Assuming triggers roll back with the statement. They do (same transaction), which is the point — but it means a trigger that throws aborts the *whole* application transaction, not just its part.
6. Row triggers on bulk DDL/data migrations. Use `COPY` with the trigger disabled where you can prove equivalence, or use set-based transition tables.
7. `ENABLE ALWAYS` triggers firing during replication or `session_replication_role = replica`, which silently disables user triggers — and then your audit log has holes exactly when you are loading data.

**Interviewer probes**
- "Your `UPDATE` on a 10M-row table took 40 minutes. What is the first thing you check?" → triggers on the table (`SELECT tgname FROM pg_trigger WHERE tgrelid = 'orders'::regclass AND NOT tgisinternal;`), then missing indexes on the join keys the trigger touches, then a missing `VACUUM` making dead tuples pile up.
- "Why did a trigger silently drop 500 rows?" → `BEFORE` trigger returned `NULL` on some path.
- "How would you make an audit log that cannot be bypassed?" → ship the WAL (logical decoding) to an append-only sink, plus a trigger for readable row-level history, plus hash-chaining for tamper evidence.
- "What is a `WHEN` clause on a trigger worth?" → It avoids the PL/pgSQL call entirely for non-matching rows, which is the expensive part.

---

# 6.9 Stored procedures, functions, and the ORM-vs-database-code line

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition.** A **stored procedure** is a named, parameterised batch of SQL executed server-side that may commit or roll back itself. A **function** returns a value and runs inside a query; in Postgres a function participates in the same transaction as its caller and cannot commit. Both live inside the database engine, so they are part of the *data model*, not part of the application.

## 6.9.1 Mechanics in Postgres

```sql
-- Function: composable, callable in SELECT, runs in the caller's transaction.
CREATE FUNCTION fn_order_totals(p_tenant bigint, p_since timestamptz)
RETURNS TABLE (customer_id bigint, orders bigint, total_cents bigint)
LANGUAGE sql STABLE PARALLEL SAFE
AS $$
  SELECT o.customer_id, count(*), sum(o.total_cents)
  FROM orders o
  WHERE o.tenant_id = p_tenant AND o.created_at >= p_since
  GROUP BY o.customer_id;
$$;

-- Procedure: can COMMIT; only callable from the top level or inside DO/CALL.
CREATE PROCEDURE pr_rebuild_monthly_rollup(p_month date)
LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
BEGIN
  DELETE FROM monthly_rollup WHERE month = date_trunc('month', p_month)::date;
  INSERT INTO monthly_rollup
  SELECT date_trunc('month', created_at)::date, tenant_id,
         sum(total_cents), count(*)
  FROM orders
  WHERE created_at >= p_month AND created_at < p_month + interval '1 month'
  GROUP BY 1, 2;
  COMMIT;   -- illegal in a function
END;
$$;
CALL pr_rebuild_monthly_rollup('2024-03-01');
```

Volatility and side-effect declarations matter because the planner uses them and misdeclaring them produces silent wrongness:

| Marker | Meaning | Planner consequence |
|---|---|---|
| `IMMUTABLE` | Same inputs, same output, no side effects, no time dependence | Indexable in expression indexes; can be inlined; can be constant-folded |
| `STABLE` | Same inputs, same output *within one statement/scan*; may read the DB | Cannot be inlined across rows; evaluated once per query where possible |
| `VOLATILE` (default when in plpgsql) | May change per call; may have side effects | Evaluated per row; not usable in an index |

The classic bug: marking a function `STABLE` when it reads `now()` or `current_setting()`, so the planner caches one evaluation for the whole query. Marking a `IMMUTABLE` function that secretly reads a table means a wrong answer no amount of `ANALYZE` will explain.

```sql
-- Language choice and its cost
-- plpgsql: procedural, BEGIN/END, exception blocks, slow to interpret per row.
-- sql:    inlinable by the planner, can become a plain subquery. Prefer it.
-- plpython3u / plperlu: C-speed numeric work, but an interpreter crash is a
--         backend crash and untrusted-language restrictions apply.
-- c:      compiled, fastest, and the maintenance cost is yours.
```

```sql
CREATE FUNCTION fn_rank_orders(p_customer bigint)
RETURNS SETOF orders
LANGUAGE sql STABLE
AS $$
  SELECT * FROM orders o
  WHERE o.customer_id = p_customer
  ORDER BY o.created_at DESC
  LIMIT 10;
$$;

-- SRF in the SELECT list (PG 10+): lateral-ish behaviour, and it explodes rows.
SELECT c.name, (fn_rank_orders(c.id)).order_id FROM customers c LIMIT 5;
-- Prefer LATERAL for clarity:
SELECT c.name, r.order_id
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN LATERAL fn_rank_orders(c.id) r ON true;
```

**PL/pgSQL control flow that actually appears in production code:**

```sql
-- Set-based, not row-by-row: a loop over a query is a silent O(n) interpreter.
CREATE FUNCTION pr_recompute_all(p_tenant bigint) RETURNS void LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
DECLARE
  r record;
BEGIN
  FOR r IN SELECT id FROM orders WHERE tenant_id = p_tenant ORDER BY id LOOP
    PERFORM 1;
  END LOOP;
  COMMIT;
END $$;

-- The same thing, correctly:
UPDATE orders o SET total_cents = s.total
FROM (SELECT id, sum(line_cents) AS total FROM order_lines GROUP BY id) s
WHERE s.id = o.id AND o.tenant_id = p_tenant;
```

```sql
-- Exception handling: a subtransaction per BEGIN/EXCEPTION block.
CREATE FUNCTION pr_safe_import(p jsonb) RETURNS text LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
DECLARE
  inserted int := 0;
BEGIN
  INSERT INTO orders(customer_id, total_cents, status)
  SELECT (e->>'customer_id')::bigint, (e->>'total_cents')::bigint, 'pending'
  FROM jsonb_array_elements(p) e
  ON CONFLICT (idempotency_key) DO NOTHING;
  GET DIAGNOSTICS inserted = ROW_COUNT;
EXCEPTION
  WHEN unique_violation THEN
    RAISE WARNING 'duplicate in batch, rolled back all % rows', inserted;
    RETURN 'duplicate';
  WHEN others THEN
    RAISE EXCEPTION 'import failed: % %', SQLSTATE, SQLERRM;
END $$;
```

Note the consequence, which interviewers like: **a PL/pgSQL `EXCEPTION` block rolls back everything inside it** (a savepoint), then continues or propagates. Partial-batch imports are a *deliberate* savepoint-per-row pattern, and each savepoint costs.

```sql
-- Per-row savepoint so one bad row does not kill the batch.
DO $$
DECLARE
  r jsonb;
  ok int;
BEGIN
  FOR r IN SELECT * FROM jsonb_array_elements($1) LOOP
    BEGIN
      INSERT INTO orders(customer_id, total_cents) VALUES ((r->>'customer_id')::bigint, (r->>'total_cents')::bigint);
    EXCEPTION WHEN others THEN
      RAISE WARNING 'row % failed: %', r->>'id', SQLERRM;
    END;
  END LOOP;
END $$;
```

## 6.9.2 Functions that are secretly tables

Postgres lets you replace a table with a function of the same name and parameter signature, which enables **LSP (leaky/stacked abstraction, "function-backed table")** patterns that a lot of teams discover by accident:

```sql
-- A table and a function with the same name: queries that say
-- `SELECT * FROM x(...)` get the function; `SELECT * FROM x` gets the table.
CREATE FUNCTION jsonb_array_elements_strict_or_empty(...)
-- and then you can define operators, or make the "table" a view over a
-- JSON document, or a foreign-data-wrapper's table, or a partitioned parent.
```

The practical version of this idea you will actually meet in an interview: **table inheritance / declarative partitioning (6.15)** lets you present one logical table over many physical ones, and **updatable views** let you present a join as a table you can `UPDATE`. Both are "make a virtual table" tricks, and both have the same hazard: the abstraction leaks on error messages, on `EXPLAIN`, and on lock behaviour.

## 6.9.3 The line: how much logic belongs in the database?

There is no universal answer, but there is a defensible one. Put logic in the database when it must be **atomic with the data**, **shared across all writers**, or **too expensive to move**:

| Put it in the database when | Put it in the application when |
|---|---|
| It must be in the same transaction as the data (derived totals, cross-row invariants, decrement-and-check) | It needs an external dependency (HTTP, cache, queue, third-party API) |
| Every writer must obey it, including ETL, admin SQL, and the next service in another language | It needs to be unit-tested with mocks, versioned with the code, and deployed independently |
| It is set-based and the database does it far faster than a row loop (bulk transforms, nightly rollups) | It is the business's product logic and the product changes weekly |
| It is an invariant you would never want optional: tenancy scoping, audit | It is presentation, validation ergonomics, or workflow orchestration |
| It must work when the data is modified by hand (`psql`) | The team is large and code review is your quality gate |

Real examples that belong in the database: `tenant_id` scoping predicates (or RLS), `updated_at`, unique constraints, exclusion constraints for no-overlap bookings, a `fn_*` that computes a derived ranking used by three different queries, an idempotent upsert with `ON CONFLICT`.

Real examples that belong in the application: anything that calls an API, anything a PM will change this sprint, anything you need to trace with a distributed trace ID, anything whose correctness depends on a library version.

**The failure modes of each side, stated honestly:**

- **Too much in the database:** `plpgsql` becomes an untested second codebase with no CI, no type checker, no linter, and a debugger you have to attach to a backend; a `FOR` loop over 10M rows runs for an hour; a schema change silently changes business behaviour that nobody reviews in the migration PR; you cannot scale the code tier independently of the data tier; version skew between app versions becomes impossible because different app versions call the *same* function with different assumptions.
- **Too much in the application:** the invariant is enforced by *convention*; a nightly `psql` script or an admin panel bypasses it; you pay a network round trip per row; you lose the ability to use a set-based statement; you reimplement database features badly (N+1, lost updates, race conditions on read-then-write).

**The pragmatic middle that survives senior review:**
1. Constraints and invariants in the schema (declarative, race-free, self-documenting).
2. Small, `STABLE`/`IMMUTABLE`, side-effect-free SQL functions for reuse across queries.
3. Set-based procedures for bulk operations and maintenance jobs.
4. Everything else in the application, with the data-access layer raw-SQL-fluent (`sqlc`, Kysely, Drizzle, `pg` with a query builder) rather than ORM-magic.
5. Never a network call inside a transaction or a procedure (6.5.7).

## 6.9.4 Transaction control inside procedures, and why it matters

Because a procedure can `COMMIT`, you can write a loop that commits per batch — the classic fix for a 200M-row backfill that otherwise holds one huge transaction (and one huge `xid`, and blocks vacuum for its entire duration):

```sql
CREATE PROCEDURE pr_backfill(p_batch int = 5000) LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
DECLARE n int;
BEGIN
  LOOP
    WITH moved AS (
      UPDATE orders SET status = 'migrated'
      WHERE id IN (SELECT id FROM orders WHERE status IS NULL ORDER BY id LIMIT p_batch FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED)
      RETURNING 1
    ) SELECT count(*) INTO n FROM moved;
    COMMIT;
    EXIT WHEN n = 0;
  END LOOP;
END $$;
```

Warnings: `COMMIT` inside a procedure means **you can no longer roll back the whole thing** — partial failure leaves partial state, so the procedure must be idempotent and must reconcile (`ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`, count-based checkpoints). It also means the caller cannot wrap it in a transaction, which breaks ORM transaction helpers. And it means `ROLLBACK` inside an exception block only rolls back to the savepoint, not the whole run.

## 6.9.5 Transactional outbox: the case for database-side logic

The one database-side component that is almost always worth it, because it makes a cross-system atomicity problem tractable:

```sql
CREATE TABLE outbox (
  id           bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  aggregate    text NOT NULL,
  payload      jsonb NOT NULL,
  created_at   timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
  published_at timestamptz
);
CREATE INDEX ON outbox (id) WHERE published_at IS NULL;  -- pending-scan index

-- In the same transaction as the business write:
INSERT INTO orders(...) VALUES (...);
INSERT INTO outbox(aggregate, payload) VALUES ('order.created', jsonb_build_object('order_id', orders.id));
```

A relay polls `SELECT ... WHERE published_at IS NULL ORDER BY id LIMIT 100 FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, publishes to the broker, then sets `published_at`. Properties: at-least-once (consumers must be idempotent, keyed on `outbox.id`), no lost events, no dual-write gap, ordering per aggregate if you only poll with a single worker per aggregate. Costs: the table grows, you must partition or archive it, the relay adds a few hundred milliseconds of latency, and polling adds database load (or use logical decoding, which is the version without the polling table).

**Common mistakes**
1. Marking a function `STABLE`/`IMMUTABLE` when it reads time or session state; or `VOLATILE` when it is pure, which prevents inlining and constant folding.
2. Row loops (`FOR r IN SELECT`) over large sets where a single set-based statement would do.
3. `RAISE NOTICE` for error handling in production (it goes to the client log, is rate-limited, and is invisible to application logs).
4. Assuming `CALL pr_...` composes with your ORM's transaction helper. It does not, once the procedure commits.
5. Procedures that take `COMMIT` and are therefore not re-runnable safely; a retry duplicates side effects.
6. Granting `EXECUTE` on functions to `PUBLIC` (the Postgres default!). Security-definer functions in particular must have explicit grants.
7. Using `plpgsql` for something expressible in `LANGUAGE sql`, losing inlining and getting a slower plan.

**Interviewer probes**
- "When would you write a stored procedure rather than application code?" → The invariant must be atomic with the data and shared by all writers; name a concrete example such as a counter decrement with `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` + `RETURNING`, or an idempotent ingest upsert.
- "Why is a plpgsql loop so much slower than an `UPDATE ... FROM`?" → Per-row interpreter overhead plus per-statement planning, versus one plan executed over the whole set.
- "Your procedure cannot be called inside a transaction. Why?" → It commits, so it cannot run inside one.
- "How do you make a database-side job restartable?" → Idempotent set-based statements, an explicit checkpoint column or `COMMIT` per batch, and reconciliation queries that compare source to target counts.
- "What is the function-backed-table trick, and when is it right?" → Presenting one logical relation over many physical ones (partitioning, inheritance, updatable views) so consumers write one query; the leak is on error messages, plans, and locks.

---

# 6.10 Connection pooling: application pools, PgBouncer, and pool exhaustion

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition.** A **connection pool** is a bounded set of pre-established client connections shared by concurrent request handlers. **Connection pooling in the database** (Postgres `pgbouncer`, MySQL `ProxySQL`, Oracle `Pooled Connection Manager`) is a process that accepts client connections and multiplexes them onto a smaller set of real server connections, in **transaction** or **session** pooling mode.

**The arithmetic that drives everything.** A Postgres backend is an OS process with its own memory: ~5 to 10 MB of private memory at rest for a trivial session, more with `work_mem`, more with parallel workers and large plans. So:

```
max_connections  <=  RAM / (per-backend cost + overhead)
```

On a 64 GB box with `work_mem = 4MB` and 8 parallel workers per query, a realistic per-backend ceiling is 250 MB under load. Practical guidance: `max_connections = 50` per app instance with a proxy in front is a common, healthy design; `max_connections = 5000` is a machine that will OOM or thrash, and no amount of tuning rescues it. MySQL's `max_connections = 1000` default is similarly a DoS surface, not a target.

**The pool size formula people argue about.** Little's Law: concurrency = throughput × latency. If a service does 2,000 requests/s and each request holds a connection for 20 ms (including its transaction), the required pool is `2000 × 0.02 = 40` connections. Queueing theory then says a pool far larger than that *reduces* throughput, because Postgres's shared-memory locks and buffer contention degrade superlinearly past a certain concurrency (roughly, useful concurrency ≈ `2 × cores` for CPU-bound work and `~ (cores × 4)` to `32 × cores` for I/O-bound work). The practical rules:

- Size the pool from **measured** `hold_time × request_rate`, not from a blog post.
- Add a small buffer for bursts and one for a maintenance slot; keep `pool_size < max_connections` with headroom for `psql`, replication slots, and background workers.
- **Set a `connectionTimeoutMillis`.** A pool with an infinite wait converts a traffic spike into a total outage.
- **Reserve capacity for the DBA.** If `max_connections` is fully consumed by app connections, you cannot log in to fix anything. Keep a reserved slot (RDS does this with `superuser_reserved_connections`, and `admin` in RDS Proxy / a separate "maintenance" pool in PgBouncer).

## 6.10.1 Application-side pools

```
Node.js / Prisma:  connection_limit = 10..20 per instance, pool_timeout = 10s
Node pg:           new Pool({ max, idleTimeoutMillis, connectionTimeoutMillis, statement_timeout })
Python SQLAlchemy: pool_size=10, max_overflow=5, pool_timeout=10, pool_recycle=1800
Java HikariCP:     maximumPoolSize = 10, connectionTimeout = 3000, leakDetectionThreshold = 60000
Go database/sql:   SetMaxOpenConns(25), SetMaxIdleConns(25), SetConnMaxLifetime(30m)
Ruby/Puma:         one DB connection per Puma thread/worker; threads = pool size
```

```js
// Postgres (node-postgres): the settings that actually matter.
import { Pool } from 'pg';
const pool = new Pool({
  host: process.env.PGHOST,
  max: 15,                          // per process; multiply by replicas
  idleTimeoutMillis: 30_000,        // close idle connections so pgbouncer can reclaim
  connectionTimeoutMillis: 5_000,   // never wait forever
  keepAlive: true,
});
pool.on('error', (err) => { /* an idle client errored: log, do not crash */ });

export async function withTx(fn) {
  const c = await pool.connect();
  try {
    await c.query('BEGIN');
    const out = await fn(c);
    await c.query('COMMIT');
    return out;
  } catch (e) {
    await c.query('ROLLBACK').catch(() => {});
    throw e;
  } finally {
    c.release(e);   // pass the error: pg destroys the connection rather than returning a suspect one
  }
}
```

```yaml
# HikariCP: the leak-detection threshold is the most valuable line here.
spring:
  datasource:
    hikari:
      maximum-pool-size: 10
      minimum-idle: 10            # keep the pool full; avoids ramp latency
      connection-timeout: 3000
      idle-timeout: 600000
      max-lifetime: 1800000       # must be below any proxy/DB idle timeout
      leak-detection-threshold: 60000
```

**Connection leak** is the number one cause of pool exhaustion. Symptoms: `pg_stat_activity` shows hundreds of `idle in transaction` or `idle` sessions whose `state_change` is old, the pool's `size` is at `max` while the queue grows, and the app's health check (which itself needs a connection) fails. Causes: a `BEGIN` with no `COMMIT`/`ROLLBACK` on an error path, a `return` inside a `try` without a `finally`, a callback that never fires, a connection handed to a queue and never released. Detectors: `leak-detection-threshold` in Hikari, pool metrics (`numWaiters`, `acquireCountMs`) in Hikari metrics/Prisma, and `SELECT count(*), state FROM pg_stat_activity GROUP BY state` with `max(clock_timestamp() - state_change)` per state.

```sql
-- Who is leaking, in Postgres
SELECT pid, usename, application_name, client_addr, state,
       clock_timestamp() - xact_start AS xact_age,
       clock_timestamp() - state_change AS state_age,
       left(query, 120)
FROM pg_stat_activity
WHERE state <> 'idle'
   OR xact_start IS NOT NULL
ORDER BY xact_age DESC NULLS LAST
LIMIT 20;
```

## 6.10.2 PgBouncer: three modes, and the rule that decides which one

| Mode | What a client session maps to | Session state supported | Latency saving | Use when |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `session` | 1:1 with a server connection for the life of the client connection | Everything: temp tables, `SET`, `LISTEN`, advisory locks, prepared statements | None; it is a connection-count limiter only | You need full session semantics, or your client library is exotic |
| `transaction` | Reassigned after every `COMMIT`/`ROLLBACK` | Protocol-level prepared statements (with `max_prepared_statements > 0`), `SET` applied at connect time only | Large: 5000 clients over 100 server connections | **The default choice for stateless web apps** |
| `statement` | Reassigned after every statement | Almost nothing; no multi-statement transactions | Largest, but unusable for writes | Statement-level proxies, some MySQL tools |

**The rule:** in `transaction` mode, anything that assumes it owns a backend for the duration of the *session* is broken:
- `SET search_path` / `SET TIME ZONE` per transaction works only if set on every transaction (`options` in the connection string, or PgBouncer's `server_reset_query` variants).
- `PREPARE`/`DEALLOCATE` need `max_prepared_statements` (PgBouncer 1.21+) or forced simple protocol. This is the single most common "it broke when we added the proxy" bug.
- `LISTEN`/`NOTIFY` and holding a cursor across transactions break.
- **Session-level advisory locks break** (the lock lives on the backend, which may change). Use `pg_advisory_xact_lock` (transaction-scoped) instead. 6.6.3 already flagged this trap.
- `WITH HOLD` cursors and multi-statement functions that read session GUCs break.
- Long-lived interactive sessions and `psql` `\gdesc`-style exploration get confused.

```ini
; pgbouncer.ini (transaction mode)
[databases]
orders = host=pg-primary.internal port=5432 dbname=orders
orders_ro = host=pg-replica.internal port=5432 dbname=orders pool_mode=transaction

[pgbouncer]
listen_addr = 0.0.0.0
listen_port = 6432
max_client_conn = 20000          # cheap: no backend per client
```
