```ini
default_pool_size = 100          # server connections per (db, user) pair
min_pool_size = 10
reserve_pool_size = 20           # only used when a client has waited > reserve_pool_timeout
reserve_pool_timeout = 3
max_db_connections = 400         # hard cap across all pools
max_user_connections = 400
server_idle_timeout = 600
server_lifetime = 3600           # recycles connections; balances DNS changes and failover
server_connect_timeout = 5
server_check_query = select 1
server_check_delay = 30
client_idle_timeout = 0          # 0 = keep forever; set it if you want the proxy to shed
ignore_startup_parameters = extra_float_digits
auth_type = scram-sha-256
auth_query = SELECT usename, passwd FROM pg_user WHERE usename=$1

[pgbouncer]
log_connections = 0
log_disconnections = 0
log_pooler_errors = 1
stats_users = ops
```

**Failover.** Point the app at the primary's DNS name. In a failover the primary's IP changes (RDS flips a CNAME; a self-managed Patroni cluster moves the VIP). PgBouncer caches DNS at connect time; `server_lifetime` plus periodic `server_check_query` are what make it recover. The alternative is running the app through a managed pooler/load balancer that re-resolves. Test failover explicitly — an untested failover path is the one that fails during the failover.

**Splitting primary and replica.** Two separate pooler endpoints (or two `[databases]` entries with `pool_mode=transaction` and a read-only user with `default_transaction_read_only=on`) is the clean approach, and it is also how you prevent a "read" that accidentally lands on the primary.

**MySQL equivalents.** MySQL's own `wait_timeout` and `max_connections` are the server-side half; the client-side half is the driver pool. Proxies: **ProxySQL** (query rules, multiplexing, hostgroups: you can route by SQL comment or user to writer vs reader), **MaxScale**, **RDS Proxy** (managed, query-aware caching of prepared statements), and **Vitess's `vtgate`** if you are already in that world. MySQL's `wait_timeout` (default 28800s) is why proxy-muxed idle connections look fine and then all die at once when the server closes them — set `wait_timeout` and the client's `keepalive` consistently, and expect "server has gone away" to mean "idle too long", not "server died".

## 6.10.3 Diagnosing pool exhaustion

The triage order that actually finds it:

```sql
-- 1. Is it the DB, or the app?
SELECT count(*) AS total,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE state = 'active')        AS active,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE state = 'idle')          AS idle,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE state = 'idle in transaction') AS idle_in_txn,
       max(clock_timestamp() - xact_start) FILTER (WHERE xact_start IS NOT NULL) AS oldest_xact
FROM pg_stat_activity;

-- 2. What are the long transactions doing?  (idle-in-transaction blocks vacuum)
SELECT pid, now() - xact_start AS age, state, left(query, 200)
FROM pg_stat_activity
WHERE xact_start < now() - interval '1 minute'
ORDER BY xact_start;

-- 3. Are you blocked on a lock?  (Postgres 9.6+)
SELECT pid, pg_blocking_pids(pid) AS blockers, wait_event_type, wait_event, left(query,150)
FROM pg_stat_activity
WHERE cardinality(pg_blocking_pids(pid)) > 0;

-- 4. Server-side wait events: where is the time actually going?
SELECT wait_event_type, wait_event, count(*), max(now() - query_start) AS slowest
FROM pg_stat_activity GROUP BY 1,2 ORDER BY 3 DESC LIMIT 20;

-- 5. Connection churn: are clients reconnecting constantly?
SELECT backend_type, state, count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity GROUP BY 1,2;
SELECT * FROM pg_stat_database WHERE datname = current_database();  -- sessions, xact_commit, conflicts
```

The distinguishing signatures:

| Symptom | Likely cause |
|---|---|
| Postgres `max_connections` reached, error `FATAL: sorry, too many clients already` | App pool size × replicas > DB capacity; no proxy |
| Pool `numWaiters` climbing, DB `active` low, latency high | Leak: connections held by long transactions or slow code paths, so the pool is full but the DB is idle |
| Pool `numWaiters` climbing, DB `active` at `max_connections`, high CPU or high `Lock` waits | Genuinely saturated database: the pool is doing its job; you need a query fix, not a pool fix |
| Every request times out at exactly the pool `connectionTimeout` value, DB looks fine | The pool is smaller than concurrency allows, and code is not releasing connections (a `finally` is missing) |
| `idle in transaction` count high | Missing rollback on an error path; this is a vacuum blocker and a slot waster |
| Latency climbs with concurrency, DB CPU flat, all waits are `Client` | Queueing in the app or the proxy: the client is slow, or the pool is thrashing |
| `pgbouncer` `sv_active`/`sv_idle` at `default_pool_size` and `cl_waiting > 0` | `default_pool_size` too low for that pool's load, or a long transaction pinning a server connection |
| Errors only after a deploy, "prepared statement does not exist" | Proxy in transaction mode without `max_prepared_statements`, or a rolling deploy mixing clients |

**Production considerations**
- **Two numbers per deployment, in the runbook:** `pool_size × instance_count ≤ pgbouncer.default_pool_size × (db,user) pairs`, and `max_connections ≥ sum(server pools) + 10 for humans + replication slots`.
- **`statement_timeout` and `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` are your safety net.** The latter is the single best Postgres setting for killing leaks: `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = '60s'`. Also `log_min_duration_statement = 500ms` so slow queries are visible, and `log_connections`/`log_disconnections` at a sampling rate while debugging churn.
- **Circuit breaker + load shedding at the DB edge.** If DB latency exceeds a threshold, fail fast rather than letting every request queue on the pool. This turns a saturation event into a controlled degradation.
- **Pool per tenant of the fleet?** No: per instance, small, behind a proxy. Huge client counts with few server connections is exactly the point of transaction-mode pooling.
- **Warm the pool** (`min_idle == maximumPoolSize` in Hikari, PgBouncer `min_pool_size`) so a traffic spike does not pay TCP + TLS + auth + planning on the critical path.
- **Test at the real concurrency.** A load test with 50 VUs proves nothing about pool behaviour at 5,000. Test connection storms: kill and restart instances and watch whether the remaining ones survive, because that is when every client reconnects at once.

**Common mistakes**
1. `connection_limit = 100` "to be safe", with 30 replicas. 3,000 connections on a 32 GB database.
2. No `connectionTimeoutMillis`/`connectionTimeout`, so exhaustion becomes a hang rather than an error.
3. Passing the error to `release(e)` omitted, so suspect connections return to the pool and fail the next borrower.
4. Using PgBouncer in `session` mode "to be safe" and wondering why the memory savings vanished.
5. Running PgBouncer in `transaction` mode with prepared statements and no `max_prepared_statements`.
6. Using session-level advisory locks behind a transaction-mode pooler (6.6.3).
7. A read replica and primary sharing one pooler entry, so 40% of reads hit the writer.
8. `max_lifetime` longer than the proxy's `server_lifetime`, so connections die in use. Keep app < proxy < DB in lifetime ordering.
9. Adding `SET` statements at connect time only, then a pooled session picks up another tenant's setting. Pass per-role settings via roles or `options`, never via ad-hoc `SET` outside a transaction.
10. Treating "increase the pool" as the fix for slow queries. It usually makes it worse.

**Interviewer probes**
- "How many database connections should a service with 100 pods have?" → Derive it: `hold_time × throughput` for concurrency, capped at a small pool per pod behind a transaction-mode proxy; explain why 10 per pod with 3,000 client connections at the proxy and ~100 server connections is healthier than 100 per pod.
- "Why can a *smaller* pool be faster?" → Queueing and lock contention: extra concurrency adds contention on shared buffers and locks without adding throughput.
- "What is `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` for?" → Killing leaked transactions that block vacuum and hold locks/snapshots.
- "Your proxy is in transaction mode and `LISTEN/NOTIFY` stopped working." → Session-bound feature; move to a dedicated connection or poll.
- "How do you test failover for the pooler?" → Kill the primary, watch DNS/IP change, watch PgBouncer reconnect within `server_lifetime` + `server_check_delay`, and confirm in-flight transactions fail fast rather than hang.

---

# 6.11 PostgreSQL specifics: JSONB, full-text search, arrays, and extensions

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition.** The four Postgres features teams reach for that other engines implement differently or not at all: `jsonb` (a binary, indexed, operator-rich document type), full-text search (`tsvector`/`tsquery` built on an inverted index), array columns (`int[]`, `text[]`), and the extension ecosystem that turns Postgres into a search engine, a time-series database, a vector database, and a queue.

## 6.11.1 `jsonb`: mechanics

```sql
CREATE TABLE events (
  id          bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  account_id  bigint NOT NULL,
  kind        text   NOT NULL,
  payload     jsonb  NOT NULL DEFAULT '{}'::jsonb
);

-- The three indexes you will actually create
CREATE INDEX ON events USING gin (payload);                       -- whole doc
CREATE INDEX ON events USING gin (payload jsonb_path_ops);        -- ~2-3x smaller, no key-exists
CREATE INDEX ON events (account_id, (payload->>'plan'));          -- B-tree on an extracted value
CREATE INDEX ON events USING gin (payload jsonb_path_ops)
  WHERE kind = 'checkout';                                        -- partial
```

**`json` vs `jsonb`:**
- `json` stores the exact input text, preserves key order and whitespace, and duplicates keys. It is diffable and readable, and it is not indexed and is compared as text.
- `jsonb` parses on write into a binary tree: keys are deduplicated (last wins) and sorted, whitespace is gone, and it is **indexable** and comparable by content. The write cost is the parse; the read cost is lower and index access works.
- Rule of thumb: **`jsonb` everywhere you query; `json` only when you are storing a verbatim document you must round-trip byte-for-byte** (an audit payload, a raw webhook body).

**The operators you need cold:**

| Expression | Returns | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `payload->'user'` | `jsonb` | Key by text |
| `payload->>'user'` | `text` | Key, extracted as text; the workhorse |
| `payload->1` / `payload->>1` | `jsonb` / `text` | Array index |
| `payload#>'{a,b}'` | `jsonb` | Path, avoids repeated `->` |
| `payload#>>'{a,b}'` | `text` | Path to text |
| `payload @> '{"a":1}'` | boolean | Contains; **GIN-indexable** |
| `payload ? 'a'` | boolean | Key exists; GIN-indexable |
| `payload ?| ARRAY['a','b']` | boolean | Any key exists |
| `payload ?& ARRAY['a','b']` | boolean | All keys exist |
| `jsonb_path_query(payload, '$.items[*].price ? (@ > 10)')` | `jsonb` set | SQL/JSON path (PG 12+) |
| `payload || '{"c":3}'::jsonb` | `jsonb` | Shallow merge; arrays **concatenate**, objects merge |
| `payload - 'a'` | `jsonb` | Remove key |
| `jsonb_set(payload, '{a,b}', '1')` | `jsonb` | Set nested, creating intermediates |
| `jsonb_pretty(payload)` | `text` | Debugging |
| `jsonb_build_object('a',1,'b',x)` | `jsonb` | Construct |
| `to_jsonb(row)` / `to_jsonb(t)` | `jsonb` | Whole row / whole table |

**Containment is the one to remember.** `@>` is the operator a GIN index accelerates, and it composes:

```sql
-- Finds any event for account 7 that is a checkout AND has a coupon.
-- One index scan, no row-by-row filtering.
SELECT id, created_at
FROM events
WHERE account_id = 7
  AND kind = 'checkout'
  AND payload @> '{"coupon": {"code": "SUMMER"}}';
```

Note: `@>` on **arrays** means "contains all elements" (`'["a","b"]'::jsonb @> '["a"]'::jsonb` is true), which is a common source of confusion with `jsonb` array-containment in the `_int` world.

**Generated columns: the escape hatch that makes JSONB queryable like a table**

```sql
ALTER TABLE events
  ADD COLUMN plan text GENERATED ALWAYS AS (payload->>'plan') STORED;
CREATE INDEX ON events (account_id, plan);
-- now: WHERE account_id = 7 AND plan = 'pro' is a plain indexed B-tree lookup
```

`STORED` generated columns are written once at insert and immutable (which is exactly why they index well); `VIRTUAL` columns (PG 18) compute on read. This is the recommended answer to "we store JSONB because the schema is flexible" — flexible on write, fast on read, because you promote the fields you actually filter and sort into typed columns.

**The `jsonb` performance reality:**
- A GIN index with default `jsonb_ops` supports `@>`, `?`, `?|`, `?&`. `jsonb_path_ops` supports `@>` and `@?` only but is far smaller (it stores hashes of keys/values, not the values themselves), so lookups are faster and the index fits in memory more often.
- Default GIN has a **pending list**: new entries go to a small pending list and are merged into the main index by `gin_pending_list_limit` (default 4 MB). So a freshly-loaded table's index is not fully searchable until autovacuum runs. Symptom: "my query misses rows I just inserted" — real, and fixed by `VACUUM` or `UPDATE`ing the rows.
- GIN writes are slower than B-tree writes. On a write-heavy table, a GIN index costs throughput.
- Extraction (`->>`) on a non-indexed path is a full scan with a filter. Never rely on it alone at scale.
- `jsonb` values above ~2 KB get TOASTed (compressed and/or moved out of line); this is transparent but makes them slower and prevents index-only scans in some cases.

**When to use `jsonb` vs when to use a real table:**

| Use `jsonb` | Use relational columns / a child table |
|---|---|
| Attributes are unbounded in *number* and rarely queried (feature flags, provider payloads, form answers) | The field is queried, filtered, sorted, or joined on |
| The shape genuinely varies per row and per tenant | The field participates in a constraint, FK, or unique rule |
| You are ingesting a third-party payload you must preserve | You need aggregates (`sum`, `avg`) over it efficiently |
| The document is read whole and never queried field-wise | Concurrent updates to different sub-fields (whole-row rewrite, no partial update) |
| An event envelope: `type`, `version`, `body` | A core entity with a stable shape |

The honest interview line: **"`jsonb` is a good answer to "we do not know yet" and a bad answer to "we do not want to write a migration"."** Every promoted field is a migration you deferred; the cost is paid the first time you need to query it, and the escape hatch (a generated column plus an index) costs one `ALTER TABLE`.

## 6.11.2 Full-text search: mechanics

```sql
-- The two synthetic columns
ALTER TABLE documents
  ADD COLUMN search_vector tsvector
    GENERATED ALWAYS AS (
      setweight(to_tsvector('english', coalesce(title, '')),    'A') ||
      setweight(to_tsvector('english', coalesce(body, '')),     'B') ||
      setweight(to_tsvector('english', coalesce(author, '')),   'C')
    ) STORED;

CREATE INDEX ON documents USING gin (search_vector);

SELECT id, title, ts_rank(search_vector, q) AS rank
FROM documents, websearch_to_tsquery('english', 'postgres full text indexing')
WHERE search_vector @@ websearch_to_tsquery('english', 'postgres full text indexing')
ORDER BY rank DESC
LIMIT 20;
```

**The analysis chain** (cross-reference 6.25): `string → parser → token types → dictionaries (stems, stopwords, synonyms) → functions → tsvector`. The `tsvector` is a sorted list of `(lexeme, positions, weights)`; the `tsquery` is the parsed query; `@@` is "matches". The GIN index stores the inverted index of lexemes, so `@@` is an index lookup, not a scan.

**Weights** `A` (title) > `B` (body) > `C` (metadata) > `D` (unweighted), used by `ts_rank`. `setweight` and the concatenation in a generated column is the standard way to express field-specific relevance.

**Query builders, in order of preference:**

| Function | Input syntax | Use |
|---|---|---|
| `websearch_to_tsquery(lang, text)` | Free text, `"quoted phrases"`, `-negation`, `OR` | User-facing search boxes. **Never concatenate raw user input into `to_tsquery`** — that is the injection-equivalent for search |
| `plainto_tsquery(lang, text)` | Plain words, AND-ed | Simple keyword |
| `to_tsquery(lang, text)` | Raw tsquery syntax (`&`, `|`, `!`, `<->`) | **Trusted input only**; a malformed string raises an error, and attacker-controlled input can be a DoS or leak |
| `phraseto_tsquery` | Words as an exact phrase | Quoted phrase search |

**Ranking.** `ts_rank(vector, query, normalization)` where normalization is 0 (raw), 1 (divides by 1+log(document length) — length penalty), 2, 3, 4 (progressive length penalties), 32 (`rank/(rank+1)` — makes the value comparable across queries). Start with 32 so scores are stable, and normalize *within* a result set if you display scores to users.

**`ts_headline` and `tsvector` sanity:**
```sql
SELECT ts_headline('english', body, q, 'StartSel=<mark>, StopSel=</mark>, MaxWords=30, MinWords=15')
FROM documents, plainto_tsquery('english', 'vector indexes') q
WHERE search_vector @@ q;
```

**Advanced features worth naming in an interview:**
- **Stored generated `tsvector` column + GIN**: fast reads, but every `UPDATE` rewrites the vector; the index is a large write cost. This is the standard choice.
- **External table + GIN index**: GIN over `to_tsvector(...)` directly, no stored column. Slower reads (the vector is computed per row), zero storage. Good for a read-mostly table you cannot alter.
- **RUM index** (`rum` extension): stores positional information, so it supports phrase search and near-instant ranking without re-scoring; bigger and slower to build.
- **Multilingual and per-row configuration:** `to_tsvector('simple', ...)` for code identifiers and URLs (`english` stems "c++" into nothing useful); a `regconfig` column so each row carries its own dictionary and the GIN index stays single-configuration.
- **Unaccent, trigram, and btree_gin for the non-lexical part:** `unaccent` in the vector expression (and a matching `unaccent` dictionary, plus `ALTER SYSTEM SET default_text_search_config`), and `CREATE EXTENSION pg_trgm` giving `gin_trgm_ops` for `ILIKE '%substring%'` and `similarity()`.
- **Prefix search**: `to_tsquery('eng:*')` is supported by GIN with `gin_pending_list_limit` caveats; the *correct* production answer for autocomplete is usually a **trigram index** or a dedicated prefix-friendly structure, because `tsquery` prefix matching can degrade.
- **Ranking is not semantic relevance.** Postgres FTS is lexical. If you need meaning, that is pgvector (6.23.3), and the honest answer is that you may need both: lexical for exact-match precision plus vectors for recall, fused with RRF (`1/(60+rank)` summed across the two result sets).

```sql
-- Hybrid search with reciprocal rank fusion
WITH lex AS (
  SELECT id, row_number() OVER (ORDER BY ts_rank(search_vector, q) DESC) AS r
  FROM documents, websearch_to_tsquery('english', $1) q
  WHERE search_vector @@ q LIMIT 100
), vec AS (
  SELECT id, row_number() OVER (ORDER BY embedding <=> $2::vector LIMIT 100) AS r
  FROM documents ORDER BY embedding <=> $2::vector
)
SELECT d.id, sum(1.0/(60 + l.r)) + sum(1.0/(60 + v.r)) AS score
FROM lex l FULL JOIN vec v USING (id) JOIN documents d USING (id)
GROUP BY d.id ORDER BY score DESC LIMIT 20;
```

**When Postgres FTS is enough vs when you need Elasticsearch:** Postgres FTS wins below roughly 10M documents with a modest QPS, when you want one transaction and one backup, and when the search is one entity type with facets you can express in SQL. Elasticsearch wins on typo tolerance (real phonetic/edge-n-gram analyzers), on per-field analyzers with complex synonym files and per-market dictionaries, on high fan-out of concurrent searches, and on relevance tuning that has outgrown `ts_rank`. The cost of leaving Postgres is a second source of truth, an ingestion pipeline, an NRT lag, and a reindex for every mapping change.

## 6.11.3 Arrays: mechanics and honest limits

```sql
CREATE TABLE post (
  id     bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  tags   text[] NOT NULL DEFAULT '{}',
  scores int[]
);

CREATE INDEX ON post USING gin (tags);                    -- ? , @> , &&
CREATE INDEX ON post USING gin (tags array_ops);          -- default for arrays; same thing
CREATE INDEX ON post USING btree (tags);                  -- rarely useful: only leading element
```

**Array operators:**

| Operator | Meaning | Index |
|---|---|---|
| `tags @> ARRAY['sql']` | Contains all of | GIN |
| `tags <@ ARRAY['a','b','c']` | Contained by | GIN |
| `tags && ARRAY['a','b']` | Overlaps | GIN |
| `tags = ARRAY[...]` | Exact | rarely indexable usefully |
| `tags[1]`, `tags[1:3]` | Element / slice | no |
| `unnest(tags)` | Expand to rows | — |
| `array_length`, `cardinality` | Length | — |
| `array_agg(x ORDER BY y)`, `array_remove`, `array_replace`, `array_position` | Manipulation | — |

```sql
-- Unnest with a lateral join (never in the target list of a plain SELECT,
-- where it silently multiplies rows in PG 10+ pre-lateral semantics)
SELECT p.id, t.tag
FROM post p, LATERAL unnest(p.tags) AS t(tag);

-- Element counts per tag
SELECT t.tag, count(*)
FROM post p, LATERAL unnest(p.tags) AS t(tag)
GROUP BY t.tag ORDER BY 2 DESC LIMIT 20;

-- Aggregate a many-to-many into one row
SELECT u.id, array_agg(p.id ORDER BY p.created_at DESC) AS post_ids
FROM users u JOIN posts p ON p.author_id = u.id GROUP BY u.id;

-- "Has any of these tags" with the tag table joined in
SELECT DISTINCT p.id
FROM post p
WHERE p.tags && (SELECT array_agg(slug) FROM tag WHERE category_id = $1);
```

**When arrays are right and when they are wrong:**

| Arrays are good | Arrays are bad |
|---|---|
| Small, bounded, write-rarely lists: tags, `int[]` of up to ~50 permission IDs, a fixed set of feature flags | Anything that grows without bound: a 10,000-element "recent activity" array is a write-amplification disaster (whole array rewritten, no partial update, TOASTed) |
| Frequently queried *as a set* with `@>`/`&&` and a GIN index | When you need per-element metadata (a tag with a weight, a vote with a timestamp) — that is a child table |
| When the list is *not* a real entity (no independent lifecycle, no FK, never queried by id) | When you need referential integrity: Postgres cannot FK an array element |
| `int[]` of enum-like codes for a fixed permission set (fast `&&` check) | When you need aggregates over elements with a join to another table |
| Replacing a many-to-many join when you accept that the reverse query is impossible | When you need a foreign table to be modified by a different service safely — the row is contended |

The right way to say it: **"an array is a set-valued attribute, not a table. If anyone will ever ask a question whose answer requires joining across elements or per-element metadata, it should be a child table."**

## 6.11.4 Extensions: what each one buys, and what it costs

| Extension | Turns Postgres into | Key capability | Cost/caveat |
|---|---|---|---|
| `pg_trgm` | Fuzzy text search | `similarity()`, `GIN trigram` indexes for `LIKE '%x%'` and typo tolerance | Large indexes on big text columns |
| `unaccent` | Accent-insensitive text | Removes diacritics in the FTS pipeline | Requires `unaccent` in `default_text_search_config`; extension must be in the DB, not just `search_path` |
| `fuzzystrmatch` | Approximate matching | `soundex`, `dmetaphone`, `levenshtein` | Cheap functions, slow on big scans without an index |
| `citext` | Case-insensitive text | A collation-insensitive type | Case-insensitive unique indexes behave surprisingly with trailing spaces; prefer `lower(col)` plus a functional unique index |
| `uuid-ossp` / `pgcrypto` | UUID and crypto | `gen_random_uuid()`, `digest()`, `pgp_sym_encrypt` | `pgcrypto` in `pg_catalog` (PG 13+); do not use DB-side encryption for high-value secrets |
| `hstore` | Legacy key-value | Fast key/value in one column | Superseded by `jsonb`; `jsonb` has the same GIN performance and a standard format |
| `btree_gin` | Mixed scalar + JSON | One GIN index over `(tags, payload)` | Sparse indexes; great for `?` + `&&` combined predicates |
| `btree_gist` | Exclusion constraints with scalars | `EXCLUDE (room_id WITH =, during WITH &&)` | The 6.6 no-overlap trick depends on it |
| `postgres_fdw` / `file_fdw` | Federated queries | Query another Postgres (or CSV) as a table | Pushdown is limited; latency per remote query; no cross-source transaction |
| `pg_stat_statements` | Query observability | Per-statement calls, total time, rows, cache hit ratio | **Turn it on**, it is the single most useful extension in production |
| `pgcrypto`'s `pgp_sym_encrypt` + `pg_trgm` on ciphertext | Encrypted fuzzy search | Trigram over ciphertext leaks nothing useful | Do not |
| `pgvector` | Vector search | HNSW, IVFFlat, cosine/L2/inner product, filters (6.23.3) | Build time, memory, recall tuning |
| `TimescaleDB` | Time-series | Hypertables, compression, continuous aggregates, retention | Hypertable DDL intercepts your table; a different planner experience |
| `PostGIS` | Geospatial | `geography`/`geometry`, spatial indexes, joins | Heavy; only if you have a genuine spatial requirement |
| `pg_cron` | Scheduled jobs in the DB | `cron.schedule` | Same warning as `pg_net`: jobs in the database are jobs nobody deploys or monitors |
| `pg_net` / `plpgsql` HTTP | Egress from the DB | Async HTTP | Do not call this from a transaction |
| `hypopg`, `pg_qualstats` | Index/plan experimentation | Hypothetical indexes, predicate selectivity | Dev-time only |
| `logical_decoding` plugins (`wal2json`, `pgoutput`) | Change streams | Publish row changes to Kafka | WAL volume cost, slot management, one slot per consumer (a forgotten slot holds the WAL and fills the disk) |

**Extension rules:**
1. Extensions are installed per-database (`CREATE EXTENSION`) but their shared library lives in `$sharedir`; managed providers allow a whitelist only. Check before you design around one.
2. `CREATE EXTENSION` requires superuser or the right privilege; RDS/Cloud SQL often pre-install a set.
3. Some extensions (TimescaleDB, Citus) change planner and DDL behaviour in ways you must understand, not just enable.
4. **`pg_stat_statements` and `auto_explain` should be on in production.** `auto_explain.log_min_duration` and `log_analyze` on slow statements give you plans for the queries that actually hurt, without waiting for a human to reproduce them.
5. `pg_repack`, `pgstattuple`, `pg_buffercache` are operational essentials, not nice-to-haves.
6. Logical replication slots are a disk risk: `max_slot_wal_keep_size` bounds it, and a monitoring alert on `pg_replication_slots.active` and slot size is mandatory.

**Common mistakes**
1. `to_tsquery($user_input)` — malformed input throws, and the query language lets a user express expensive things. Use `websearch_to_tsquery` or `plainto_tsquery`.
2. `payload->>'field'` in a `WHERE` clause with only a GIN index on the whole document and no expression index, discovered at 10M rows.
3. Storing UUIDs as `text`.
4. `array_agg` into a column that grows forever, then wondering why `UPDATE`s on the parent became slow.
5. Forgetting that GIN inserts go to a pending list, so freshly-inserted rows are not found until vacuum.
6. Comparing text with `=` while assuming case-insensitivity from a UI expectation. Postgres `=` is byte-exact and collation-aware for ordering but not for equality. Use `citext`, `lower()`, or `ILIKE`.
7. Declaring a `tsvector` expression `IMMUTABLE` when it depends on `default_text_search_config` (which is a session GUC), then getting plan-cache surprises across differently-configured sessions.
8. Enabling `pg_stat_statements` and never looking at it.
9. A logical replication slot with no consumer, discovered when the primary's disk is 95% full.

**Interviewer probes**
- "How would you make a JSONB document column as queryable as a normal table?" → Promote the hot fields to `STORED` generated columns with B-tree indexes, plus a `jsonb_path_ops` GIN for containment queries; answer with the migration cost honestly.
- "Why is `websearch_to_tsquery` safer than `to_tsquery`?" → It does not accept the tsquery operator syntax, so arbitrary input cannot produce an expensive or malformed query; it never errors.
- "When is an array the wrong choice?" → When it grows unbounded, needs per-element metadata or FKs, or must be queried from the other side.
- "What is the GIN pending list and when does it bite?" → New entries accumulate in a small pending list until autovacuum merges them; recently inserted or updated rows can be invisible to index-only search until then.
- "Which extensions would you turn on in production on day one?" → `pg_stat_statements`, `auto_explain`, `pg_trgm`, and the observability set (`pg_buffercache`, `pgstattuple`, `pg_repack` installed but not scheduled).

---

# 6.12 MySQL and other dialects: what actually differs

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition.** A map of the differences that actually break code when it moves from Postgres to MySQL (or to SQLite, SQL Server, Oracle). Not a feature matrix — a list of places where the *same SQL* means a *different thing* or has a *different cost*.

## 6.12.1 The differences that bite hardest

| Topic | PostgreSQL | MySQL 8 / InnoDB | Consequence |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Default isolation** | `READ COMMITTED` | `REPEATABLE READ` (gap locks) | Two apps behave differently on the same code. MySQL's snapshot is taken at the *first read*, not at the transaction start statement |
| **`SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` on a non-existent row** | Nothing is locked; you get a "phantom" on insert | Gap/next-key locks lock the gap, blocking inserts | Optimistic "check then insert" patterns deadlock under MySQL |
| **MVCC undo** | One `xmin` chain per row; long transactions block vacuum and xid wraparound freeze | Read view + undo log; `innodb_purge` cleans up; long transactions hold the read view and prevent purge, which grows undo | Same "long transaction is dangerous" story, different tables to look at |
| **`SELECT` without `FOR UPDATE`** | Sees the latest committed row version | Sees the snapshot as of the transaction's first read | Two different "read your own writes" behaviours |
| **DDL transactional** | Yes, almost all of it | **No.** DDL causes an implicit commit and cannot be rolled back | A failed `ALTER TABLE` leaves the schema half-migrated; no transactional migration on MySQL, so migrations must be idempotent by construction |
| **`ALTER TABLE` cost** | Usually metadata-only (adding a nullable column with no default is instant) | Rebuilds the table for most changes; `ALGORITHM=INPLACE` vs `COPY` | A MySQL migration on a 100M-row table is a multi-hour table copy plus a lock at the end (the `COPY` algorithm takes `LOCK TABLES`) |
| **`ADD COLUMN ... DEFAULT <volatile>`** | PG 11+ stores the default in the catalog; no rewrite | MySQL 8.0.13+ supports instant for some cases; otherwise rebuild | Test on your exact version |
| **Identity / auto-increment** | `GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY`, sequences independent of the table | `AUTO_INCREMENT`, counter inside InnoDB, per-table, gaps on rollback, cached in memory | Sequence-vs-table behaviour affects sharding and failover |
| **UUID type** | Native `uuid`, 16 bytes, orderable | No native type; `CHAR(36)`, `BINARY(16)` via `UUID_TO_BIN`, or `VARCHAR` | B-tree fragmentation with v4 on both, but no `uuidv7()` helper |
| **Case-insensitive identifiers/strings** | Case-sensitive | Identifiers case-sensitive (depends on `lower_case_table_names`), **strings case-insensitive by default** (`utf8mb4_0900_ai_ci` collation) | `WHERE name = 'Bob'` matches `'bob'`. Joins are likewise case-insensitive: indexes are used but comparisons cost collation work |
| **`NULL` in unique indexes** | Multiple NULLs allowed (unless `NULLS NOT DISTINCT`, PG 15+) | Multiple NULLs allowed | Same |
| **Upsert** | `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT (col) DO UPDATE` | `INSERT ... ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE`, or `INSERT IGNORE` | `INSERT IGNORE` swallows *all* errors (data truncation too), not just duplicates; a classic data-corruption footgun |
| **CTEs** | Full support, `WITH ... AS MATERIALIZED`/`NOT MATERIALIZED`, recursive | Full support in 8.0, recursive supported | MySQL sometimes materializes a CTE you hoped would be inlined |
| **Window functions** | Full, all frames | Full in 8.0 | Same |
| **Lateral** | `LATERAL` supported | `LATERAL` supported in 8.0.14+ | Same |
| **`SELECT` locking clause** | `FOR UPDATE [OF tbl] [NOWAIT|SKIP LOCKED] [NOWAIT]` | `FOR UPDATE [SKIP LOCKED]` (no `NOWAIT`) | Workaround for `NOWAIT`: `innodb_lock_wait_timeout = 1` or `NOWAIT`-emulation via `GET_LOCK` |
| **Advisory locks** | `pg_advisory_lock`, session and transaction variants | `GET_LOCK`/`RELEASE_LOCK` (session-scoped, **no transaction variant**, released only on explicit release or disconnect) | A MySQL advisory lock leaks if the connection is returned to a pool; the `pg_advisory_lock` trap from 6.6.3 is *worse* here |
| **Skip-locked queue pattern** | `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` | Same | The work-queue pattern is portable |
| **Savepoints** | Yes | Yes, but MySQL cannot roll back a *transaction* inside a savepoint-release path cleanly for some engines | Mostly fine |
| **Text types** | `text`, `varchar(n)`, `char(n)`; unlimited `text` | `TINYTEXT`/`TEXT`/`MEDIUMTEXT`/`LONGTEXT` (64KB/16MB/4GB); row size limit 65,535 bytes; `TEXT` off-page storage | Two `TEXT` columns in an index-prefix context need care |
| **Row size** | TOAST, effectively no practical limit (1 GB per field) | Hard 65,535-byte row limit, `BLOB`/`TEXT` stored off-page with a 20-byte pointer | Wide rows fail at DDL time on MySQL, silently TOAST on Postgres |
| **Enum type** | Enum is a `CHECK` on text; no native enum | Native `ENUM('a','b')`, stored as an integer, hard to change (needs a table rebuild) | Prefer a lookup table or a `CHECK` constraint; both engines |
| **Boolean** | Native `boolean` | `TINYINT(1)` alias; still `NULL`-able three-valued logic | Same gotchas |
| **JSON** | `json`/`jsonb`, indexed with GIN, operators everywhere | `JSON` (binary) with `JSON_*` functions: `JSON_EXTRACT`, `JSON_CONTAINS`, `JSON_SET`, `JSON_TABLE` (8.0) | Syntax is entirely different; MySQL's functional indexes (8.0.13+) can index `JSON_UNQUOTE(JSON_EXTRACT(...))` |
| **Full-text search** | `tsvector`, GIN, stemming per language, ranking | Built-in `FULLTEXT` on MyISAM/InnoDB, natural-language and boolean mode, **no stemming** (`ngram` parser for CJK), relevance is a count-based score | Weaker; MySQL 8.0's InnoDB full-text has no ranking function worth the name, so you sort by `MATCH() AGAINST()` ascending or post-sort |
| **Trigram / fuzzy** | `pg_trgm` | None built in | Application or Elasticsearch |
| **Arrays** | Native `int[]`, `text[]`, GIN | No native array (only `JSON`); multi-row `INSERT` with `VALUES (...),(...)` | Genuine gap; MySQL shops use JSON or a child table |
| **Ranges** | Native `int4range`, `tstzrange`, exclusion constraints | No native range type | Gaps; the no-overlap booking pattern needs `btree_gist` equivalent logic, which means a lock-based or generated-column approach |
| **Generated columns** | `GENERATED ALWAYS AS (...) STORED`, and `VIRTUAL` in PG 18 | `GENERATED ALWAYS AS (...) [VIRTUAL|STORED]`, indexed in 8.0 | Comparable |
| **`RETURNING`** | Yes, on `INSERT`/`UPDATE`/`DELETE` | Not supported in MySQL at all | This is a big one: `INSERT ... RETURNING id` must become `LAST_INSERT_ID()`, which is connection-scoped and therefore pool-hostile |
| **`DELETE`/`UPDATE` with a join** | Subquery required; Postgres has no `UPDATE ... JOIN` | `UPDATE t1 JOIN t2 ON ... SET ...` native | Dialect fork in the data layer |
| **`TRUNCATE`** | `TRUNCATE t RESTART IDENTITY CASCADE` | `TRUNCATE t` (implicit commit, resets auto-increment) | Same implicit-commit hazard on both |
| **`AUTO_INCREMENT` guarantees** | Sequences can be cached (`CACHE n`) and lose values on failover | `innodb_autoinc_lock_mode` (default 2, "interleaved"), gaps guaranteed, values consumed on rollback | Both produce gaps; neither guarantees contiguity |
| **Partitioning** | Declarative range/list/hash + `ATTACH PARTITION`, `pg_partman`; partitioned tables have no unique indexes across partitions (PG < 17? — verify; PG 15+ requires the partition key in the unique key) | `PARTITION BY RANGE/LIST/HASH/KEY`, `ALTER TABLE ... ADD PARTITION`, subpartitioning | Same conceptual model; MySQL requires all unique keys to include the partitioning column, same as Postgres |
| **Replication** | Streaming, logical | Statement/row/mixed binary log + `GTID` | Both fine; MySQL's read-only replicas need `super_read_only` to be truly read-only |
| **Group replication / consensus** | Patroni + etcd, `synchronous_standby_names` | InnoDB Cluster (Group Replication), semi-sync replication | Both offer automatic failover with a consensus layer |
| **Connection proxy** | PgBouncer | ProxySQL, MaxScale, RDS Proxy | — |
| **Types** | `numeric` arbitrary precision, `timestamptz`, `interval`, `int8` range | `DECIMAL`, `DATETIME`/`TIMESTAMP` (no tz; `TIMESTAMP` semantics changed in 5.7!), `BIGINT` | Store UTC in `DATETIME`, convert at the edges; never store local time |
| **`INTERVAL`** | Rich: `interval '1 day'`, months vs days distinct (month = calendar) | `INTERVAL` works but `DATE_ADD`/`DATE_SUB` arithmetic differs in edge cases (month-end clamping, `DAY_HOUR` compound units) | Date math bugs live here |
| **Serialization of `LIMIT` in `UPDATE`** | Not allowed | Not allowed | — |
| **`GROUP BY` strictness** | Postgres allows selecting columns functionally dependent on the group key (recognizes `GROUP BY` on the PK) | `ONLY_FULL_GROUP_BY` is on by default in 5.7+ | MySQL will reject a query Postgres accepts |
| **Identifier quoting** | `"` | Backtick, or `"` if `ANSI_QUOTES` | Dialect fork |
| **Comments / hints** | `/*+ */` only by extension | `SELECT /*+ MAX_EXECUTION_TIME(1000) */`, `/*+ INDEX(t idx) */` | MySQL has real optimizer hints; Postgres relies on `SET LOCAL` |
| **Query timeout** | `statement_timeout`, `lock_timeout`, `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` | `max_execution_time` (SELECT only), `innodb_lock_wait_timeout`, `lock_wait_timeout` | Postgres's set is more complete and applies to all statement types |
| **`pg_stat_activity` equivalent** | `pg_stat_activity` with wait events, `pg_stat_statements` | `performance_schema` (`events_statements_*`, `sys` views), `information_schema.PROCESSLIST`, `SHOW ENGINE INNODB STATUS` | MySQL's is more verbose and harder to use for the "what is blocking me" question (`sys.innodb_lock_waits` exists) |
| **Vacuum** | Autovacuum per table, `xmin` wraparound freeze, `VACUUM FULL` takes `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` | `innodb_purge` thread + adaptive purge lag; `OPTIMIZE TABLE`/`ALTER TABLE ... ENGINE=INNODB` rebuilds | Same story: a long transaction blocks purge; `information_schema.INNODB_TRX` is your `pg_stat_activity` |
| **Kill a query** | `pg_cancel_backend` / `pg_terminate_backend` | `KILL QUERY` / `KILL CONNECTION` | Both easy; both needed in a runbook |
| **Temp tables** | Session-scoped, dropped at disconnect | Session-scene, but `ON COMMIT DELETE ROWS` inside a transaction is a good pattern | — |
| **Views** | Auto-updatable for simple views; `security_barrier` | `MERGE` vs `TEMPTABLE` algorithm; `ALGORITHM=MERGE` is updatable | Same conceptual model |
| **Triggers** | Row and statement, `WHEN`, transition tables, `CONSTRAINT TRIGGER` deferred | Row and statement, no `WHEN` clause (use `BEGIN IF` inside), no transition tables | MySQL triggers cannot modify the table they are on (raises error 1442), and there is no `UPDATE OF col` |
| **Sequences** | First-class objects, `nextval`/`setval`, `CACHE`, `ALTER SEQUENCE` | Emulated with a table or `AUTO_INCREMENT`; `SETVAL` via `ALTER TABLE ... AUTO_INCREMENT = n` | Portable sequence generation: a `counters` table with `UPDATE ... RETURNING`-equivalent, or accept `AUTO_INCREMENT` |

**Other dialects, briefly:**

- **SQLite**: single-writer, whole-database locking (`BEGIN EXCLUSIVE`), no `ALTER COLUMN`, `WITHOUT ROWID` tables for small integer keys, WAL mode for concurrent readers with one writer, `PRAGMA journal_mode`, FTS5 as a module, and a hard ceiling of practical concurrency that makes it an embedded/mobile/CLI store, not a server store.
- **SQL Server**: clustered vs nonclustered indexes (clustered **is** the table), `READ_COMMITTED_SNAPSHOT` (row versions, reader-writer non-blocking) vs locking, `NOLOCK` being a bug not a feature, `MERGE` being dangerous, `IDENTITY`, and cross-database/server queries via `OPENROWSET`/`OPENQUERY`. Snapshot isolation at the database level (`ALLOW_SNAPSHOT_ISOLATION`) is the setting everyone forgets.
- **Oracle**: `NUMBER`, `VARCHAR2`, empty string *is* NULL, `NVARCHAR2` for the national character set, PL/SQL packages, no `LIMIT` (uses `ROWNUM`/`OFFSET`), sequence-per-table (identity columns only from 12c), and `SYSDATE` being transaction-stable while `SYSCOMMIT` is wall-clock.

## 6.12.2 The portable-SQL strategy

1. **Pick one dialect for the app and write raw SQL through a thin query layer** (`sqlc`, Kysely, Drizzle, `pg` + a query builder) so the dialect lives in one place.
2. **Standardize the type mapping in the driver**: `timestamptz` ↔ `DATETIME` (store UTC), `jsonb` ↔ `JSON`, `uuid` ↔ `CHAR(36)`/`BINARY(16)`, `boolean` ↔ `TINYINT(1)` (or `INT` if 3-valued logic matters, because `TINYINT` can hold values other than 0/1 if your code is careless).
3. **Avoid dialect-only syntax in the hot path**: no `UPDATE ... JOIN`, no `INSERT IGNORE`, no `GROUP BY` on non-aggregates, no `DATE_ADD`/`INTERVAL` in business logic (do date math in the app or use a library).
4. **Abstract the migration tool, not the SQL**: Flyway, Liquibase, or a custom runner that emits engine-specific DDL from one declarative schema definition. Never rely on a MySQL migration being transactional.
5. **Test the semantics, not just the results.** A test that asserts "returns 3 rows" can pass on both engines while the *concurrency* behaviour is completely different. Add a test that runs two transactions concurrently and asserts the isolation behaviour you depend on.
6. **Know the 6.12.1 blockers that force an engine choice**: no arrays, no `RETURNING`, no range types or exclusion constraints, weaker FTS, no DDL transactions. If your design needs `EXCLUDE ... WITH &&` for booking, that is a Postgres decision.

**Model answer, "Postgres or MySQL":** "Context: a multi-tenant B2B SaaS, 8,000 tenants, roughly 400 million order-line rows, complex reporting, and a team of 12 engineers, none of whom have run a distributed Postgres. Postgres: better constraints (exclusion, `CHECK`, `NOT NULL` that actually hold), `jsonb` with GIN, real FTS, range types, expression indexes, `RETURNING`, transactional DDL which makes migrations safe, and window functions everywhere. MySQL: simpler operational story, managed autoscaling and failover that is genuinely one click, `ProxySQL` for read/write splitting, and a team-support argument that is real. Decision: Postgres for the schema and constraints, because the hardest bugs in this domain are duplicate-booking and lost-update bugs, and only Postgres gives me declarative answers to both. Trade-offs: MySQL's managed failover and RDS Proxy's caching would save me a platform engineer's time, and MySQL's optimizer is more predictable on a small number of very large simple queries. Failure scenario: we pick MySQL for the operational win and then need a `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` gap-lock-free optimistic insert for booking; we end up hand-rolling a lock table and a distributed lock, which is worse than the boring declarative solution."

**Interviewer probes**
- "Your Postgres migration failed halfway. What happened, and what is different on MySQL?" → Postgres DDL is transactional so it rolled back; on MySQL each DDL is an implicit commit, so the schema is partially migrated and your migration must be idempotent and resumable.
- "Why is `INSERT IGNORE` dangerous?" → It converts *any* error, including data truncation and constraint violations it did not intend, into a warning, so you lose rows silently.
- "How do you get the inserted id on MySQL?" → `LAST_INSERT_ID()`, which is per-connection, so it must be read on the same connection as the insert, which leaks it into the data-access layer.
- "Your MySQL deadlock rate is high. Why?" → Gap locks under `REPEATABLE READ`, and inconsistent index usage in concurrent multi-table updates. Fixes: index every join/filter key, keep transactions short and in a consistent table order, consider `READ COMMITTED`.
- "What breaks if you move a Postgres app to MySQL?" → The dialect list above; the ones that silently corrupt are case-insensitive string comparison, `INSERT IGNORE`, missing `RETURNING`, and non-transactional DDL.

---

# 6.13 Replication and high availability: sync vs async, read replicas, failover

🔟 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition.** Replication is the process of maintaining one or more copies of a database and streaming changes to them. **High availability** is the property that the system accepts reads and writes during any single component failure. **Synchronous** replication waits for a replica to acknowledge before committing; **asynchronous** replication acknowledges as soon as the primary's local log is durable, and may lose the last writes if the primary dies.

## 6.13.1 Streaming replication in Postgres

Postgres has three replication modes and they answer different questions:

| Mode | What is streamed | Lag | Use |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Physical (WAL streaming)** | Byte-level WAL, applied by `pg_receivewal` or a replica's `walreceiver` | Low, usually sub-second on the same AZ; seconds cross-region | Read replicas, PITR base images, DR warm standbys, upgrades with `pg_upgrade --link` |
| **Logical** | Row-level changes decoded from WAL by a plugin (`pgoutput`, `wal2json`, `pglogical`) | Depends on the subscriber | Zero-downtime major version upgrades, CDC to Kafka/other engines, multi-master-ish fan-out (with conflict handling), selective table sync |
| **Snapshot only** | `pg_dump`/`pg_basebackup` file copy | Hours | Backups, not replication |

```sql
-- On the primary: WAL settings
ALTER SYSTEM SET wal_level = replica;         -- minimum for streaming; 'logical' for logical decoding
ALTER SYSTEM SET max_wal_senders = 10;
ALTER SYSTEM SET max_replication_slots = 10;
ALTER SYSTEM SET wal_keep_size = '4GB';        -- retain WAL so a replica can catch up
ALTER SYSTEM SET archive_mode = on;            -- required for PITR
ALTER SYSTEM SET archive_command = 'test ! -f /mnt/wal/%f && cp %p /mnt/wal/%f';
ALTER SYSTEM SET synchronous_commit = on;      -- default; per-transaction can be relaxed
ALTER SYSTEM SET synchronous_standby_names = 'ANY 1 (replica1, replica2)';  -- PG 10+
SELECT pg_reload_conf();
```

```ini
# postgresql.conf on the replica
primary_conninfo = 'host=pg-primary.internal port=5432 user=replicator application_name=replica1 sslmode=verify-full'
primary_slot_name = 'replica1_slot'      # prevents WAL removal on the primary
hot_standby = on                         # allows reads while recovering
max_standby_streaming_delay = 30s       # how long to keep trying before cancelling a query
wal_receiver_status_interval = 10s
hot_standby_feedback = on                # replica tells the primary its xmin; prevents vacuum removing needed rows
```

**Lag measurement, and the right lag metric.** `pg_stat_replication` gives `write_lag`, `flush_lag`, `replay_lag` — the *write*, *flush*, and *replay* components, measured from timestamps embedded in the WAL. They are the honest way to measure: `sent_lsn - replay_lsn` on the primary, or `pg_last_wal_replay_lsn()` on the replica, gives the byte distance.

```sql
-- Replica: true end-to-end delay in seconds
SELECT now() - pg_last_xact_replay_timestamp() AS replay_delay;
-- Primary: per-replica detail
SELECT application_name, state, sync_state,
       pg_wal_lsn_diff(sent_lsn, replay_lsn) AS bytes_behind,
       write_lag, flush_lag, replay_lag
FROM pg_stat_replication;
```

`pg_last_xact_replay_timestamp()` measures delay since the last *committed* transaction, so on an idle primary it grows to the point of being useless; pair it with the byte distance and with a heartbeat: a replica replaying no WAL for an hour during a write outage is fine, and a replica 5 MB behind during a heavy write burst is not.

**Synchronous vs asynchronous, precisely:**

- **Async** (`synchronous_standby_names = ''`): commit returns after the primary flushes its WAL locally. Zero added latency. **On primary failure you lose everything not yet replicated** — measured in milliseconds to seconds under load.
- **Sync** (`synchronous_standby_names = 'ANY 1 (r1, r2)'`): commit returns after *one* of the listed standbys has flushed the WAL to its own disk (`remote_flush`, the default). Adds one network round trip to the write path. If **no** standby is connected and you have not set `synchronous_commit = local`, **the primary blocks indefinitely**, waiting for a standby. This is a real and deliberate design (Postgres will not acknowledge a write it has not replicated, so no data loss), but it turns a replica failure into a primary outage.
- **Quorum sync** (`ANY 2 (r1, r2, r3)` or `FIRST 2 (...)`): requires k of n standbys. `ANY` is better for latency (any of the set flushes), `FIRST` gives a designated primary standby. Costs an extra RTT for the k-th ack and reduces write availability.
- **`synchronous_commit = remote_apply`**: waits until the standby has *applied* the transaction, so a failover loses nothing at all — at the cost of a round trip plus replay latency, and a write can be rolled back on the replica if it is promoted mid-flight (read-only transaction failures).
- **`synchronous_commit = local`**: never wait for standbys, even if configured. Useful as an emergency override during a replica incident, and dangerous as a permanent setting.
- **Degradation policy that people actually use:** normally async; a `synchronous_standby_names` that requires *zero* standbys (`ANY 0 (...)`) so a replica outage does not block writes; accept that RPO is "the last few seconds" and make the backup/PITR story cover it. Or: sync for a *specific* table or write path by setting `synchronous_commit = remote_flush` per session for the payments service and leaving it local everywhere else. That per-workload choice is the senior answer.

**A concrete model answer on HA architecture:**
"Context: booking platform, 99.95% availability SLO, RPO 0 tolerated for payments but 30 seconds acceptable for catalogue reads, RTO 2 minutes. Design: two Postgres primaries in one region behind a consensus-managed VIP (Patroni + etcd, or a managed multi-AZ cluster). WAL streaming to two synchronous standbys for the payments schema, one async standby for read scaling, and continuous WAL archiving to object storage for PITR (6.14). Traffic goes through PgBouncer in transaction mode against the primary; reads go to the async standby, with a read-your-writes override by routing the writer's session to the primary for 5 seconds after a write. Failover: Patroni promotes the healthiest standby, updates the VIP, and PgBouncer reconnects within `server_lifetime`. Trade-offs: synchronous replication costs a network RTT per write and makes writes unavailable if both standbys are gone; the alternative is a single primary plus heavy reliance on PITR, which is cheaper and gives an RPO equal to the WAL archive interval. Failure scenario: a bad deploy issues a `DROP TABLE` and it propagates synchronously to both standbys in milliseconds; replication is not a backup, so PITR is what actually saves us, and that is why the restore drill in 6.14.4 is a scheduled calendar item."

## 6.13.2 Read replicas: routing, lag, and consistency

The routing decision is per-request, and it must be an explicit policy, not "the ORM points at a replica URL":

| Route to primary | Route to replica |
|---|---|
| Any write | Long, complex, cacheable read queries |
| The request that just wrote, for read-your-writes (route by session/user for N seconds) | Reporting, analytics, exports, backfills |
| Reads that follow a write in the same request | Any read where a documented staleness budget applies |
| Authorization, entitlement, balance, price, inventory reads | Anything where an N-second lag is acceptable and stated |
| Reads inside a transaction that has written | — |

```sql
-- A common, honest implementation: primary until the session's write TTL expires
-- and a read hint has been recorded, then replicas.
```

```ts
// Query routing policy
type Route = 'primary' | 'replica';
export function routeFor(req: Request): Route {
  if (req.method !== 'GET') return 'primary';
  if (req.headers.get('x-replica-ok') !== '1') return 'primary';  // explicit opt-in
  const lastWrite = readLastWriteAt(req);
  return lastWrite && Date.now() - lastWrite < READ_YOUR_WRITES_MS ? 'primary' : 'replica';
}
```

**Replica lag and its user-visible consequences:**
- **Read-your-writes violation**: user updates their profile, is redirected, and sees the old profile. Fix: route that session to the primary for a window, or (better) write through a cache and read from it, or use a "version/token" where the client sends the version it last saw and the read is `WHERE version >= token`.
- **Replication lag spikes** during large `UPDATE`s, index builds, `VACUUM`, checkpoints, or when the standby's disk is slow. A synchronous replica that falls behind stalls the primary's commits: `synchronous_commit` waits for the standby's flush, so a slow disk on one replica takes down writes on the whole cluster. That is a real, well-documented failure mode and it is why standby disk must be provisioned like the primary's, not like a backup's.
- **Hot standby conflict**: a read on a replica that has been open longer than `max_standby_streaming_delay` is cancelled (error 40001). Long analytical queries need `max_standby_streaming_delay = 0` (never cancel) plus careful read-only handling, or run on a separate logical-replica analytics cluster.
- **`hot_standby_feedback`** is a two-edged sword: it prevents vacuum on the primary from removing rows a standby query still needs, but a long-running query on the replica therefore blocks vacuum on the primary and can cause bloat and, eventually, wraparound problems. Turn it on for correctness, monitor `pg_stat_database` age of the oldest `xmin`, and keep replica queries short.

```sql
-- Wraparound guard: the metric that pages you
SELECT datname, age(datfrozenxid) AS xid_age FROM pg_database ORDER BY 2 DESC;
SELECT max(age(xmin)) FROM pg_stat_activity;  -- long-running query blocking vacuum
```

## 6.13.3 Failover: automatic, manual, and the decision either way

**Automatic failover (Patroni + etcd/consul):**

| Step | Detail |
|---|---|
| Detect | The leader's `primary_conninfo` connection fails; Patroni's watchdog plus a health-check query (`SELECT 1` on a warm connection) marks it unhealthy |
| Timers | `ttl` (leader lease, e.g. 30s) must be > twice the network RTT to the DCS, and `loop_wait` + `retry_timeout` govern the retry cadence |
| Promote | The highest-priority, most-advanced standby is promoted; `pg_ctl promote` triggers recovery end |
| Reconfigure | Patroni updates the DCS; every member reconfigures `primary_conninfo`; the VIP (if using one) or the DNS record is moved |
| Application | PgBouncer / the client's DNS re-resolves and reconnects. In-flight transactions on the old primary fail with `FATAL: the database system is in recovery mode` or a connection reset |
| Fencing | The old primary must be fenced (STONITH, cloud API detach, or a `recovery.conf`-style trigger file) or you risk two primaries and split brain. Patroni does this with `on_primary_start` and a `CREATE_REPLICATION_SLOT` guard, but you must verify your infrastructure supports fencing |

**Manual failover** (when you want a human in the loop, which you want for a data-corruption incident):
1. Verify the primary is truly down (`pg_isready`, host-level check, not just the proxy).
2. On the candidate standby: `pg_ctl promote -w` (or `SELECT pg_promote(true, 60)`).
3. Confirm `SELECT pg_is_in_recovery()` is `false` and that `pg_last_wal_replay_lsn()` is close to the old primary's `pg_current_wal_lsn()`.
4. Update the DNS/VIP and the application's connection string (with the TTL already lowered *before* the incident, which is the step everyone forgets).
5. Fail back deliberately later, after the root cause is fixed: resync the old primary (re-`basebackup` it, or re-join as a replica), and only then plan a switchover, ideally during a quiet window. **Failback without a resync loses every write made on the new primary.**

**RTO/RTO arithmetic to say out loud:**
- Automatic failover with Patroni: RTO 30 to 60 seconds, dominated by the leader lease.
- Managed (RDS Multi-AZ, Cloud SQL HA, AlloyDB, Aurora): RTO 60 to 120 seconds, more opaque, fewer knobs.
- Cross-region failover with a DNS change: RTO 3 to 10 minutes, dominated by DNS TTL propagation and human decision time.
- A manual `pg_ctl promote` with no runbook: RTO "unknown, probably 20 minutes".

**Failover testing is the single most valuable reliability activity**, and it must include: killing the primary process, killing the host (power-off, not `kill -9`), cutting the network between primary and standby, losing quorum in the DCS (the split-brain scenario: does Patroni refuse to promote because it cannot get a majority lock? it must), and losing a standby while the primary is under load (does the primary stall if `synchronous_commit` is `remote_flush`?).

**Multi-region topologies:**

| Topology | RPO | RTO | Cost/latency | Note |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Single region, 1 primary + 2 sync standbys | 0 | 30-60s | Low | Default for most apps |
| Single region, 1 primary + async standby in another region | Seconds | 60s (intra-region failover) or minutes (cross-region) | Cross-region async bandwidth | DR copy; often just WAL archiving |
| Active-passive multi-region (standby region with a warm standby) | Seconds to a minute | 5-15 min (failover) | Double the infra, cold caches in the DR region | Right for regulatory data residency |
| Multi-primary (Citus, CockroachDB, Spanner, Vitess) | 0 per region with serializable; conflicts possible with weaker settings | Seconds | Complexity, cross-region write latency | Only when global write locality is a hard requirement |
| Read replicas per region + one write region | Seconds | Seconds (reads), minutes (writes) | Simple, and covers the common "global reads, single-writer" case | Very common and underrated |

**Common mistakes**
1. Believing replication is a backup. A `DROP TABLE` in `autocommit` replicates everywhere in milliseconds. Backups are the answer (6.14).
2. Not monitoring `pg_stat_replication` lag with an alert. Silent replica death is discovered during an incident.
3. Under-provisioned standby disks, so synchronous commit stalls the primary. Synchronous replication makes the standby's disk speed a write-latency dependency of the primary.
4. Routing reads to a replica immediately after a write, breaking read-your-writes.
5. `synchronous_standby_names` requiring one standby with no `ANY 0` fallback, so a replica failure takes down writes.
6. Failing back without a resync, losing data written on the new primary.
7. DNS TTL of 300 seconds set at deploy time and "lowered before the incident" never actually done.
8. Testing failover in staging, where the replicas are idle and the WAL is a trickle. Failover behaviour depends on load and on `max_connections` being reached.
9. Logical replication slots with no lag monitoring: the subscriber is the *publisher's* disk problem, and a stalled subscriber will fill the primary's disk. Alert on `pg_replication_slots` `wal_status` = `extended`/`unreserved` and slot size.
10. Assuming a replica is read-only-safe. Some applications need a "read-only replica" to be writable for local temp state; MySQL needs `super_read_only` to stop even `super` users, and replicas that local dev writes to will diverge.

**Interviewer probes**
- "Sync or async replication?" → Depends on the RPO per data class: sync for money movement, async elsewhere, with the write-path availability cost named.
- "How do you prevent split brain?" → Consensus (etcd/consul) with a majority lease and real fencing (STONITH or a cloud API call), and the test is losing quorum and confirming no promotion happens.
- "Your writes slowed by 200ms after enabling synchronous replication." → One RTT per commit plus a replica flush; the fix is `ANY` (any standby) rather than `FIRST`, moving the standby to the same AZ, or relaxing to `remote_write`/`local` for non-critical paths.
- "How do you know a failover worked?" → `pg_is_in_recovery()` false on the new primary, replay LSN caught up to the last known primary LSN, application error rate and p99 back to baseline, and a successful synthetic write+read with a fencing assertion.

---

# 6.14 Backups, point-in-time recovery, and the restore drill nobody runs

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition.** A **backup** is a copy of the data at some point in time. A **restore** is the act of turning a backup back into a running database. **Point-in-time recovery (PITR)** is restoring to an arbitrary moment between the last full backup and the failure, by replaying WAL (Postgres) or binlog (MySQL) up to a target time or LSN. A **restore drill** is a rehearsed restore with a measured RTO.

**The principle to lead with in an interview: you do not have a backup until you have restored it.** Untested backups are a hypothesis. Every serious engineer can produce a backup; far fewer can produce a restore under time pressure.

## 6.14.1 Postgres backup types

| Type | Tool | What it captures | Restore time | Use |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Logical, custom format** | `pg_dump -Fc` | SQL/COPY data, schema; consistent snapshot; can be selective | Hours for TBs (parallel restore helps) | Dev/CI seeding, small DBs, schema-only migrations |
| **Logical, directory + parallel** | `pg_dump -Fd -j 8` | Same, parallelizable | Faster | Medium DBs |
| **Logical, plain SQL** | `pg_dump -Fp` | Human-readable SQL | Slowest | Auditing, diffing, review |
| **Physical, base backup** | `pg_basebackup -Ft -z -X stream`, or `pgBackRest` | Full copy of the data directory, block-level, plus WAL | Fastest (minutes to hours by size; parallel restore) | Production DR, PITR base image |
| **Physical, incremental** | `pgBackRest` (full/diff/incr), `barman` | Block-level deltas | Very fast | Large DBs with frequent backups |
| **Storage-level snapshot** | EBS/ZFS/LVM snapshot + WAL archive | A crash-consistent image of the volume | Fast | Supplement, never instead — a snapshot is only valid with its matching WAL |
| **Continuous archiving** | `archive_mode` + `archive_command` to object storage | The WAL stream that makes PITR possible | n/a | Always on in production |

```bash
# Logical, selective, with a consistent snapshot (repeatable-read to avoid mixed FK states)
pg_dump -Fc -Z 6 --file=orders_$(date -u +%Y%m%dT%H%M%SZ).dump \
  --dbname=orders --table='public.orders*' --table='public.customers' \
  --no-owner --no-acl
# Verify it is readable, not just present:
pg_restore -l orders_20240601T000000Z.dump | head -40

# Physical base backup (with a replication slot so WAL is retained)
pg_basebackup -D /var/lib/postgresql/17/basebackup \
  -Ft -z -P -X stream -R -S basebackup_slot \
  -h pg-primary.internal -U replicator
```

**Verification, which is not optional:**
```sql
-- Nothing in core Postgres verifies a backup's contents.
-- Answer: restore it to a scratch instance and diff. Automate it nightly.
```

```bash
# Automated verification: restore into a throwaway instance and run invariants
pgbackrest --stanza=db verify                       # checks repo integrity + WAL continuity
pgbackrest --stanza=db --type=backup diff           # compares a backup to the current db
barman-cloud-backup verify                          # verifies against the catalog
```

## 6.14.2 WAL archiving and PITR

```
Full backup at 02:00 ────────────────────────────────────────► failure at 14:37
   (base image)            WAL segments archived continuously  ┊
   archive_command copies each completed segment to object     ┊  replay to
   storage (S3/GCS with versioning + immutability)             ┘  14:36:59
```

```bash
archive_command = 'aws s3 cp %p s3://pg-wal/$HOSTNAME/%f --sse-kms aws:kms=<key> --only-show-errors'
restore_command = 'aws s3 cp s3://pg-wal/$HOSTNAME/%f %p --sse-kms aws:kms=<key> --only-show-errors'
# Retry forever, with a bounded attempt count; a failed archive_command fills the pg_wal
# directory and takes the primary down, so alerting on it is a P1.
```

```bash
# Restore, step by step (this is what goes in the runbook)
# 1. Provision a same-or-newer major version instance with the same extensions.
# 2. Restore the base backup.
pgbackrest --stanza=db --target-time='2024-06-01 14:36:59-00' restore
# 3. Post-restore: recovery.signal + restore_command in postgresql.auto.conf
touch "$PGDATA/recovery.signal"
echo "restore_command = 'aws s3 cp s3://pg-wal/$HOSTNAME/%f %p'" >> "$PGDATA/postgresql.auto.conf"
echo "recovery_target_time = '2024-06-01 14:36:59-00'"    >> "$PGDATA/postgresql.auto.conf"
echo "recovery_target_action = 'promote'"                  >> "$PGDATA/postgresql.auto.conf"
echo "recovery_target_inclusive = on"                      >> "$PGDATA/postgresql.auto.conf"
pg_ctl -D "$PGDATA" start
# 4. WATCH THE LOG. recovery_target_time reached -> server promotes and accepts writes.
#    Confirm: SELECT pg_is_in_recovery();  SELECT pg_last_wal_replay_lsn();
```

**`recovery_target_*` options, and the footgun:**
- `recovery_target_time = '...'` — stop just before a timestamp. **Timestamps are timezone- and clock-skew-sensitive**, which is why `recovery_target_lsn` (a byte position, unambiguous) or `recovery_target_xid` (a transaction id) are safer when you know the exact transaction.
- `recovery_target_name = 'some_restore_point'` — the best option, because you *named* the point. Create one deliberately before a risky migration:

```sql
SELECT pg_create_restore_point('before_drop_orders_20240601');
SELECT pg_get_wal_dump...  -- later: SELECT * FROM pg_walfile_name(pg_current_wal_lsn());
```

- `recovery_target_inclusive = on` — include the transaction at the target. `off` stops just before it.
- **The footgun:** if the recovery target is never reached (a wrong timestamp, missing WAL segment), the server replays to the end of the archive and then **promotes anyway** (`recovery_target_action = promote` is the default in PG 12+). You then have a database at the wrong point and may not notice. Mitigation: set `recovery_target_action = 'pause'`, check `pg_last_wal_replay_lsn()` against the expected LSN, then `pg_wal_replay_resume()`; and record the target LSN before the incident (the primary's `pg_current_wal_lsn()` at failure time, from monitoring, is the anchor).
- **The second footgun:** a missing WAL segment in the middle of the archive. The restore stops with `requested WAL segment ... has already been removed` or `could not find file ... in archive`. You must supply it (from a replica's WAL, from a cross-region archive, or from a longer-retained tier) or you restore to a later point than you intended.

## 6.14.3 Retention, storage, and the encryption/compliance layer

| Policy dimension | Reasonable default | Reasoning |
|---|---|---|
| Full backups | Daily (or weekly for large DBs with daily incrementals) | RPO baseline |
| Incremental/differential | Daily incremental, weekly full | Keeps restore time bounded |
| WAL archiving | Continuous, retained **longer than the longest outage you could plausibly need to recover from** | PITR is bounded by WAL retention, not by the last full backup |
| WAL retention tiers | 7 days hot, 35 days warm, 13 months cold (S3 Glacier) | Balances cost against "someone found a bug from March" |
| Backups of *stubs* | Object-store versioning + object-lock (compliance mode) | Ransomware deletes backups too; immutability is the control |
| Cross-region copy | Yes, daily + WAL streaming to the DR region | A regional outage must not remove both the primary and its backups |
| Encryption at rest | Backups encrypted with a KMS key distinct from the volume key | A separate key means a compromise of the DB host does not expose backups; key deletion becomes a real (if painful) kill switch |
| Access | Break-glass role, audited, separate from the app role | Backups contain everything, including PII |
| Verification | Automated restore + invariant checks, nightly; human drill, quarterly | Untested backups are hypotheses |

```bash
# pgBackRest: the config that expresses the policy
[global]
repo1-path = s3:s3.amazonaws.com/pg-backups/db
repo1-cipher-pass = ${PG_BACKREST_CIPHER_PASS}
repo1-retention-full = 4
repo1-retention-diff = 14
repo1-retention-archive-type = full
repo1-retention-archive-copy = 14
process-max = 4
start-fast = yes

[global:archive-push]
process-max = 2

[db]
pg1-path = /var/lib/postgresql/17/main
pg1-socket-path = /var/run/postgresql
pg1-port = 5432
pg1-host = pg-primary.internal

[db:backup]
pg1-archive-time = 02:00:00
pg1-archive-async = yes            # don't block commit on a slow upload
pg1-backup-type = full             # pgBackRest schedules incr/diff automatically
```

## 6.14.4 The restore drill

**The drill, in order, timed, with a written result.** This is the part nobody runs and the part that saves you.

```
PREPARE (weekly, 30 min)
  - Pick a target: "restore orders to 2024-06-01 14:36:59 into a scratch instance"
  - Confirm the archive contains every segment between base backup and target
  - Record the expected LSN and row counts from monitoring (the acceptance criteria)

EXECUTE (quarterly, timed with a stopwatch)
  t+00  Provision a scratch instance, same major version, same extensions
  t+05  Restore base backup (pgbackrest restore --target-time=...)
  t+20  Post-restore config, recovery.signal, start, watch the log to promotion
  t+35  Verify: pg_is_in_recovery() = false
        row counts per critical table vs the pre-incident recorded values
        CHECK constraints still valid: SELECT count(*) FROM each table WHERE NOT (constraints hold)
        application smoke test against the restored instance (read-only)
        pg_sequences / sequence values sane for writes (setval if restoring to an older point)
  t+45  Record actual RTO, what broke, what was missing from the runbook

REPORT (30 min)
  - Update the runbook with what you learned; add the missing monitoring
  - Fix the retention policy if the archive was incomplete
```

**What people discover, every time:**
- An extension is missing on the restore instance and the restore fails at the end. Fix: bake the extension list into the provisioning script and diff it against `pg_extension` in the runbook.
- Sequences are behind after a PITR restore: rows exist with IDs above `last_value`. The application then tries to insert and hits a duplicate key. Fix: after restore, `SELECT setval(...)` from `max(id)` for every sequence; automate it as a post-restore step. (`pgbackrest` has `--archive-mode` and there is no automatic sequence fixup in PG; on PG 15+ consider identity columns with `GENERATED BY DEFAULT` and be explicit anyway.)
- The disk is too small for the restored database plus WAL replay. Fix: size scratch instances at 1.3x the production data size.
- The `restore_command` credentials are for a role that can read the bucket but not the KMS key. Fix: test the credential path separately in the drill.
- Object-lock/WORM retention silently prevents the drill from deleting its scratch artifacts. Fix: exempt the drill account or namespace.
- Nobody knows the target LSN. Fix: continuous monitoring that records `pg_current_wal_lsn()` every 10 seconds, so "restore to just before statement X" is a lookup, not an investigation.

**Common mistakes**
1. Believing a successful backup job means a restorable backup.
2. No `recovery_target_action = pause` plus verification, so a mis-specified target silently promotes at the wrong point.
3. `archive_command` failures not alerting: `pg_wal` fills, the primary shuts down to avoid data loss, and now you have an outage *and* no fresh backups.
4. Backups in the same region/account as the primary, with credentials that the application can read.
5. No cross-region copy and no immutable/object-locked backups.
6. Restoring only the database and not the *roles, grants, extensions, and settings*: a restored instance where the app cannot log in. Fix: version the roles in IaC (`pg_dumpall --globals-only`), and keep `postgresql.conf` in IaC too.
7. No `archive_timeout`: if the write volume is low, a partially-filled 16 MB WAL segment may not be archived for a long time, so your PITR resolution in quiet periods is coarse. Set `archive_timeout = 300s` so a segment is forced out at least every 5 minutes.
8. Never rehearsing a *partial* restore (one schema) even though that is the most common real request.
9. Treating PITR as a production failover plan: PITR to a new instance takes as long as the restore drill says, which is often longer than the SLO.

**Interviewer probes**
- "How do you know your backups work?" → Automated nightly restore + invariant checks plus a quarterly timed human drill, with an RTO measured and published.
- "Give me the recovery procedure for 'an engineer ran `DROP TABLE orders` at 14:36:59 and it committed'." → Provision a scratch instance, `pgbackrest --target-time='2024-06-01 14:36:59-00'` with `recovery_target_action=promote`, replay WAL from the base backup, verify the table exists with the expected row count and the recorded LSN, then copy just that table (or the row delta) back to production with an explicit reconciliation; the full database is not restored over production because that would lose 14:37's real writes.
- "What is `archive_timeout` for?" → Bounding PITR granularity during low-write periods, so WAL segments are forced out at a fixed cadence rather than waiting to fill.
- "Why is a volume snapshot not a backup?" → It is crash-consistent only, only recoverable with the matching WAL, only as good as the snapshot's replication across regions, and it is trivially deleted along with the volume by an attacker or an errant script.

---

# 6.15 Partitioning and sharding in relational databases

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition.** **Partitioning** splits one logical table into multiple physical tables *inside one database*, so queries and maintenance touch only the relevant pieces. **Sharding** splits data across *multiple databases* (nodes), which is the only way to get past one machine's CPU, RAM, and disk I/O.

The distinction to hold onto for interviews: **partitioning buys you manageability and pruning; sharding buys you capacity.** They solve different problems and are not substitutes.

## 6.15.1 Declarative partitioning in Postgres

```sql
CREATE TABLE events (
  id          bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY,
  tenant_id   bigint NOT NULL,
  created_at  timestamptz NOT NULL,
  kind        text   NOT NULL,
  payload     jsonb  NOT NULL DEFAULT '{}'::jsonb,
  PRIMARY KEY (created_at, id)                 -- partition key MUST be in every unique key
) PARTITION BY RANGE (created_at);

CREATE TABLE events_2024_06 PARTITION OF events
  FOR VALUES FROM ('2024-06-01') TO ('2024-07-01');
CREATE TABLE events_2024_07 PARTITION OF events
  FOR VALUES FROM ('2024-07-01') TO ('2024-08-01');
CREATE TABLE events_default PARTITION OF events DEFAULT;   -- the safety net: see below

-- Attach an existing table (faster than CREATE ... PARTITION OF for big tables)
CREATE TABLE events_2024_08 (LIKE events INCLUDING ALL);
ALTER TABLE events ATTACH PARTITION events_2024_08
  FOR VALUES FROM ('2024-08-01') TO ('2024-09-01');
```

**Partition strategies:**

| Strategy | Syntax | Best for | Cost |
|---|---|---|---|
| `RANGE` | `PARTITION BY RANGE (ts)` | Time-series, retention by dropping months | You manage boundaries; must pre-create or use a default partition |
| `LIST` | `PARTITION BY LIST (region)` | Small, stable set of values (region, plan tier, status) | New value requires a new partition (or the default) |
| `HASH` | `PARTITION BY HASH (id)` | Even distribution on a hot table, load spreading | Cannot prune by time; no easy "drop old data"; rebalancing requires moving rows |
| Composite | `PARTITION BY RANGE (created_at, id)` or subpartitioning | Time plus even spread | More complexity, more planning time |

```sql
-- Subpartitioning: monthly outer, 4 weekly inner
CREATE TABLE events PARTITION BY RANGE (created_at);
CREATE TABLE events_2024_06 PARTITION OF events
  FOR VALUES FROM ('2024-06-01') TO ('2024-07-01')
  PARTITION BY RANGE (id);
CREATE TABLE events_2024_06_w1 PARTITION OF events_2024_06
  FOR VALUES WITH (MODULUS 4, REMAINDER 0);   -- hash subpartitions
```

**Pruning, and how to prove it:**
```sql
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT * FROM events
WHERE created_at >= '2024-06-15' AND created_at < '2024-06-20';
--  Seq Scan on events  (rows=0)
--    ->  Seq Scan on events_2024_06  (actual rows=412)
--          Filter: ...
--    ->  Seq Scan on events_2024_07  (actual rows=0)   <- pruned
-- Only the partitions that can contain matching rows are scanned.
```
Pruning happens at plan time using constraint exclusion (constant-folding the partition bounds) and at execution time. Verify with `EXPLAIN` for **every** query shape: pruning does not happen for a query where the partition key is wrapped in a function the planner cannot constant-fold, e.g. `WHERE date_trunc('day', created_at) = '2024-06-15'` prunes nothing, while `WHERE created_at >= '2024-06-15' AND created_at < '2024-06-16'` prunes to one partition. This is the most common "I partitioned and it got slower" cause.

**The `DEFAULT` partition decision.** A default partition guarantees inserts never fail because someone forgot a month. It is also a trap: if a row lands in the default partition that belongs in a future month, **creating that month requires scanning and moving the default partition's rows while holding an `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` lock**, and `ATTACH PARTITION ... CHECK` takes that lock. If the default has accumulated data, your "just add next month" operation is an outage. Options: (a) default partition plus an alert on its row count, (b) no default plus a strict "create partitions ahead" automation with `pg_partman` or a scheduled job, (c) default plus a monitoring alert that fires above, say, 0 rows. Industry practice is (b) with (a) as an emergency valve, or `pg_partman` with `pre_create = 7 days` and `retention` set.

```sql
-- Adding a future partition without blocking (PG 11+ has a shorter lock; PG 12+ can be
-- done in a transaction that does not hold ACCESS EXCLUSIVE for long)
BEGIN;
CREATE TABLE events_2024_09 PARTITION OF events
  FOR VALUES FROM ('2024-09-01') TO ('2024-10-01');
COMMIT;
```

**Retention by partition drop is the whole point:**
```sql
DROP TABLE events_2023_01;                 -- instant, no row-by-row DELETE, no bloat, no vacuum
-- vs
DELETE FROM events WHERE created_at < '2024-01-01';  -- hours, millions of dead tuples,
                                                     -- WAL for every row, vacuum to follow,
                                                     -- and the table does not shrink on disk
```
