This is the strongest practical argument for partitioning: **dropping a partition is a metadata operation; deleting the rows is a data operation.** If you need to delete 30% of a table regularly, partition it.

**Partitioning trade-offs, honestly:**

| Cost | Detail |
|---|---|
| No unique indexes across partitions | Every `UNIQUE` constraint must include the partition key. `UNIQUE (tenant_id, slug)` is impossible on a `RANGE (created_at)` partitioned table. Workaround: a separate global unique index table (`CREATE UNIQUE INDEX ON slug_map (slug)`), a hash partition on the same key, or an advisory lock |
| No foreign keys *from* a partitioned table to another table in PG 12+ | Supported since PG 12 (both sides must be partitioned for FKs *to* a partitioned table). PG 11 and earlier: no FKs referencing or from partitioned tables |
| Planning overhead | More partitions = more planning time. Thousands of partitions per table degrade planning; `plan_cache_mode` and prepared statements help. Keep partitions in the low hundreds per table, or use a two-level scheme |
| No `pg_dump` of "everything" as one unit | You must dump the parent (which recurses) — fine — but restoring per-partition is more work |
| Cross-partition uniqueness requires application help | See above |
| Default partition lock hazard | See above |
| Index maintenance | Every partition has its own index; more objects to monitor, more to REINDEX |
| Query flexibility | A query without a partition-key predicate scans every partition. Partitioning makes an unfiltered query *slower* |

**When to partition:** retention/deletion dominates; the table is very large (>100M rows or >50 GB); time-bounded queries dominate and pruning is measurable; you need to swap or archive a whole time window cheaply; index maintenance (REINDEX/VACUUM FULL) is unbearably long on the whole table. **When not to:** the table is small; queries never filter on the partition key; you need cross-partition uniqueness and have no workaround; you have not measured that the single table is actually a problem (add the right index first, and check `pg_stat_user_tables` for `n_live_tup`, `n_dead_tup`, `seq_scan`, `last_autovacuum`).

## 6.15.2 MySQL partitioning

```sql
CREATE TABLE events (
  id BIGINT NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
  tenant_id BIGINT NOT NULL,
  created_at DATETIME NOT NULL,
  payload JSON NOT NULL,
  PRIMARY KEY (id, created_at)              -- partition key must be in every unique key
) PARTITION BY RANGE COLUMNS (created_at) (
  PARTITION p2024_06 VALUES LESS THAN ('2024-07-01'),
  PARTITION p2024_07 VALUES LESS THAN ('2024-08-01'),
  PARTITION pmax    VALUES LESS THAN (MAXVALUE)   -- acts like DEFAULT
);
ALTER TABLE events ADD PARTITION (PARTITION p2024_08 VALUES LESS THAN ('2024-09-01'));
ALTER TABLE events DROP PARTITION p2024_06;        -- instant
ALTER TABLE events TRUNCATE PARTITION p2024_07;    -- instant
```

MySQL's differences that matter:
- **No `DEFAULT` partition keyword**: use a catch-all `VALUES LESS THAN (MAXVALUE)` and `REORGANIZE PARTITION` to split it. `REORGANIZE PARTITION pmax INTO (PARTITION p2024_08 VALUES LESS THAN ('2024-09-01'), PARTITION pmax VALUES LESS THAN (MAXVALUE))`.
- **Only one partition can have `MAXVALUE`/the catch-all**, and it must be last.
- **Partition pruning requires the partition key in the `WHERE` clause** with a constant or a range the optimizer can evaluate; a function of the column (`YEAR(created_at) = 2024`) defeats it.
- **No foreign keys on partitioned InnoDB tables** (before MySQL 8.0; still restrictive in 8.0).
- **Only one partitioning scheme per table**, but subpartitioning exists: `PARTITION BY RANGE COLUMNS (...) SUBPARTITION BY HASH(...) SUBPARTITION BY 4`.
- `ALTER TABLE ... DROP PARTITION` requires `ALTER TABLE ... DROP PARTITION` and, in some MySQL versions, the partition must not be the only one and `partitions_in_use` (InnoDB) must be off.

## 6.15.3 Sharding: the decision, the keys, and the mechanics

**Shard on the primary access pattern.** The choice of shard key is the choice of your data locality: every query you can answer without cross-shard coordination is a query that scales linearly, and every query that must fan out is a query with a latency floor equal to the slowest shard plus a merge.

| Requirement | Sharding strategy |
|---|---|
| Tenant isolation, per-tenant limits, noisy-neighbour prevention, per-tenant restore/backups | **Shard by tenant** (`tenant_id` hash or directory). Every tenant's data is one shard; all queries are single-shard |
| High-volume single-tenant writes (a giant customer) | **Shard by tenant then sub-shard** that tenant by a secondary key (time, id range); or "directory" shards with a placement table so the giant tenant gets its own shard |
| Global entities with high write rate | **Shard by entity id** with a consistent-hash or directory mapping |
| Time-series ingest | **Shard by time range** (monthly partitions per shard) plus a hash of the entity to spread write load within a month |
| Multi-region | Shard by region with a home-region writer per key, plus async cross-region replication |
| Anything needing cross-entity transactions | **Do not shard**; keep it in one relational database and accept single-node capacity, or use a distributed SQL engine that hides it |

**Consistent hashing vs range sharding:**

| | Range sharding (`id BETWEEN a AND b`) | Consistent hashing (virtual nodes) |
|---|---|---|
| Lookup | Compute from the key, or binary search a directory table | Hash the key, walk the ring, `O(log n)` nodes to check |
| Rebalancing | Move a contiguous range: efficient, but you cannot move a small amount | Move only the keys whose ownership changed: minimal data movement |
| Hot spot | A range boundary that attracts traffic (e.g., sequential IDs all landing on one shard) | Random distribution, but a *specific hot key* still hot; add a shard suffix or a cache |
| Hot-key problem | Same | Same |
| Directory metadata | Simple ranges, easy to reason about | A version-striped hash ring (`ketama`), stored in a coordination service or in each client |
| Failure isolation | A bad node takes a contiguous key range (a whole month, one tenant block) | Spread |

```ts
// Consistent hashing with virtual nodes (the "ketama" approach)
class ConsistentHash {
  constructor(private replicas = 160) {}          // ~150 vnodes per physical node
  private ring: Array<{hash: number; node: string}> = [];
  add(node: string) {
    for (let i = 0; i < this.replicas; i++) {
      this.ring.push({ hash: hashPoint(`${node}#${i}`), node });
    }
    this.ring.sort((a, b) => a.hash - b.hash);
  }
  locate(key: string): string {
    const h = hashPoint(key);
    // binary search for the first ring point >= h, wrapping to the start
    let lo = 0, hi = this.ring.length - 1;
    while (lo < hi) { const mid = (lo + hi) >> 1; (this.ring[mid].hash < h) ? lo = mid + 1 : hi = mid; }
    return this.ring[lo].node;
  }
}
```

**The shard-key rules to recite:**
1. The shard key must be **present in every query's predicate** you expect to be frequent. A missing shard key means a fan-out to all shards (a scatter-gather), whose latency is the max shard and whose load is N× the data.
2. Distribution must be **even**. Measure with the real distribution, not uniform: `SELECT tenant_id, count(*) ... GROUP BY 1` and look at the top-1% share.
3. It must be **immutable**. Changing a shard key value means moving the row.
4. It must **avoid hot spots**. Sequential ids hash unevenly only if the hash is bad; but if you shard by `id % N`, the newest ids all hit one shard at the right edge — hence uuid/random ids or a salt.
5. It must be **compatible with your transaction boundaries**. Cross-shard transactions are either avoided or made asynchronous (a saga).

**Cross-shard operations and what they cost:**

| Operation | Pattern | Cost/latency |
|---|---|---|
| Read by shard key | Direct | 1 RTT |
| Read without shard key | Scatter-gather (fan out to all shards, merge in the app or a coordinator) | N parallel RTTs (so roughly 1 RTT if truly parallel) + merge cost + N× read load |
| Aggregate across shards | Pre-aggregate per shard (`GROUP BY` returns partial results), then merge | Partial result size, not row count — this is the scalable pattern |
| Global count/ordering | Per-shard partials + a merge, or a maintained counter/matview per shard | Approximate counts are often fine; exact global `ORDER BY ... LIMIT 10` requires pulling each shard's top K |
| Join across shards | Avoid. Pre-join at write time (denormalize), or colocate on the same shard key | Broadcast-join is O(N) and usually wrong |
| Write spanning shards | Saga: local transactions + compensating actions, orchestrated | Eventual consistency, idempotency required, no rollback |
| Re-sharding | Dual-write or a change-data-capture (CDC) copy + backfill + cutover + verification | Hours to days; a migration, not a config change |
| Global unique ID | Snowflake (time + machine + sequence), `uuidv7`, or a central ID service | Central service is a SPOF; Snowflake needs careful clock-skew handling |

**Tools that hide the sharding** (so you can defer the decision): Citus (Postgres extension, distributes tables by shard key, coordinator + workers, `create_distributed_table`, `colocate`), CockroachDB, YugabyteDB, Vitess (MySQL, `vttablet`/`vtgate`), PlanetScale (Vitess), TiDB, Google Spanner, AWS Aurora DSQL (not available everywhere; check). The honest framing: **these give you one logical relational API over many nodes, which removes most of the operational cost of sharding, but they do not remove the fundamental constraint: cross-shard transactions are either slow or impossible, and the shard key still determines your scalability ceiling.**

**Model answer, "should we shard":** "Context: an events table growing 2 TB/month, 40k writes/s peak, plus a reporting workload. Current single-node ceiling: 32 GB RAM and NVMe give us maybe 60k sequential writes/s and about 3 TB before vacuum and index maintenance become unbearably long; we will hit that in five months. Decision: **partition before sharding**. First, `PARTITION BY RANGE (created_at)` with monthly partitions and a 13-month retention policy via `DROP TABLE`, which handles the retention problem (deleting 30% of the table monthly is currently our biggest vacuum/WAL cost) and makes time-bounded queries prune to one partition. That buys us six to nine months for zero architectural risk. Second, measure: if we exceed one node, we shard by `(tenant_id)` — 99% of our queries are tenant-scoped, so this makes the common path single-shard and the reporting path a fan-out that runs off-peak against replicas. Trade-offs: cross-tenant admin/reporting queries become scatter-gathers; tenant-level data locality means one giant tenant is a hot shard, so I would add a directory table that lets that tenant be split by time sub-shard; and re-sharding becomes a project, so I want the shard key decision to be one I can change (directory-based rather than pure hash). Failure scenario: we shard by `tenant_id` and discover that the top 5 tenants are 40% of volume; the directory table plus per-tenant time sub-sharding handles it, but only because we chose a directory rather than a hardcoded hash. Alternative: Citus, which would save the operational work, but it introduces a coordinator, a coordinator failover story, and a second thing to be on call for."

**Common mistakes**
1. Partitioning by a key the queries never filter on, so pruning never happens and every query got slower.
2. Forgetting the partition key must be part of every unique constraint, then discovering you cannot enforce global uniqueness of `slug`.
3. Letting the `DEFAULT`/`MAXVALUE` partition accumulate rows, then hitting a lock when adding the next month.
4. Using `DELETE` for retention instead of `DROP PARTITION`.
5. Sharding before measuring, and sharding on `id` when all queries filter by `tenant_id`.
6. Assuming a scatter-gather is free. It is N× the load and it will melt the coordinator.
7. Doing a global transaction across shards with 2PC because "the ORM makes it easy", then discovering a coordinator SPOF and a latency floor of 2 RTTs minimum plus a lock manager.
8. Re-sharding by dual-writing without a verification step, ending up with silent divergence.
9. Not planning for **resharding of a single hot tenant** — the design should permit splitting one tenant's data across shards.
10. Believing a distributed SQL engine removes the need to pick a locality key. It removes the operational work, not the fundamental constraint.

**Interviewer probes**
- "Partition or shard?" → Partition for retention and pruning on one node; shard when you exceed one node's CPU/RAM/I/O. Partition first, because it is reversible and cheap.
- "How do you enforce a unique constraint on a range-partitioned table?" → Include the partition key in the unique key, or keep a separate global unique index table, or add a computed hash sub-key to the unique constraint.
- "Your partition has 5000 sub-partitions and planning got slow." → Planning cost scales with partition count; consolidate to two levels (range by month, hash by 16) and raise `plan_cache_mode` / use prepared statements.
- "How do you avoid the giant-tenant hot shard?" → Directory-based placement with per-tenant sub-sharding on a secondary key; or virtual-node consistent hashing with per-key shard suffixes.
- "What breaks cross-shard consistency?" → Nothing "breaks"; it just becomes a saga with idempotent consumers and explicit compensation, and the interview answer should say that the alternative (a single database) is correct until a measured limit forces the move.

---

# 6.16 Zero-downtime schema evolution and migration safety

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition.** A schema change that is applied to a live system serving traffic, without errors, without downtime, and without data loss. Because you cannot stop the world to change the schema, the deployment is a **compatibility negotiation between old and new application code**: every moment, some requests are handled by old code and some by new code, and the schema must satisfy both.

**The core principle: expand, migrate, contract.** Three deploys, not one.

```
Deploy 1  EXPAND     Add the new column/table/index. Old code unaffected.
                    Deploy new code that writes BOTH and reads OLD (or new with fallback).
Deploy 2  MIGRATE    Backfill in batches. Verify. Switch reads to new.
                    Deploy new code that reads NEW, still writes both.
Deploy 3  CONTRACT   Stop writing old. Drop the old column/index/table.
```

## 6.16.1 The lock matrix: what each DDL actually does in Postgres

| Operation | Lock | Rewrites table? | Safe on a hot table? |
|---|---|---|---|
| `ADD COLUMN` nullable, no default | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` but **metadata-only, near-instant** | No | Yes |
| `ADD COLUMN ... DEFAULT <constant>` | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE`, metadata-only (PG 11+) | No | Yes |
| `ADD COLUMN ... DEFAULT <volatile>` (e.g. `now()`) | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE`, **full rewrite** (PG 10 and earlier; PG 11+ avoids it if the default is volatile? — verify: PG 11+ stores non-volatile defaults without a rewrite, and still rewrites for volatile ones) | Yes | No — use add-then-backfill |
| `ADD COLUMN ... NOT NULL DEFAULT x` | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` | Rewrite (older versions); PG 11+ with a non-volatile default is fast | Careful — see the 3-step trick below |
| `ALTER COLUMN TYPE` (widening: `varchar(50)` → `varchar(100)`, `int` → `bigint`) | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` but **metadata-only if no rewrite is needed** | Sometimes avoidable | Yes for the widening cases |
| `ALTER COLUMN TYPE` (narrowing or changing type family, e.g. `text` → `int`) | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` | **Full rewrite + index rebuild** | No |
| `SET NOT NULL` | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE`, scans the table to validate | No rewrite, but a full scan under the lock | No on a big table — use the `NOT VALID` CHECK trick |
| `DROP NOT NULL` | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE`, metadata-only | No | Yes |
| `CREATE INDEX` | `SHARE` (blocks writes, allows reads) | n/a | Only on a small/quiescent table — use `CONCURRENTLY` |
| `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` | `SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE` (allows reads and writes) | n/a, but **two table passes and it cannot run in a transaction** | Yes, but it is slow and it can leave an `INVALID` index on failure |
| `DROP INDEX` | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` on the index's table | No | Yes, but queries depending on it immediately degrade |
| `ALTER TABLE ... ADD CONSTRAINT ... FOREIGN KEY` | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` on both tables, plus validation scan | No rewrite | Small tables only; use `NOT VALID` |
| `ALTER TABLE ... ADD CONSTRAINT ... FOREIGN KEY NOT VALID` | Brief `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE`, then `SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE` for validation | No | Yes — existing rows are not checked |
| `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` | `SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE` on both tables | No rewrite | Yes, online |
| `ALTER TABLE ... ADD CHECK (...) NOT VALID` | Brief `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` | No | Yes |
| `ALTER TABLE ... VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` | `SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE` | No | Yes, online; concurrent index build is skipped while running |
| `SET (fillfactor = 90)` | `SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE`, no rewrite | No | Yes |
| `ALTER TABLE ... SET (autovacuum_enabled = false)` | `SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE` | No | Yes |
| `ALTER TABLE ... RENAME COLUMN` | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE`, metadata-only | No | Yes, **but breaks old code immediately** — the rename is the breaking change, not the lock |
| `ALTER TABLE ... SET DEFAULT` | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE`, metadata-only | No | Yes |
| `ALTER TABLE ... ATTACH/DETACH PARTITION` | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` briefly (PG 12+ shorter) | No | Yes, but takes the lock on the parent |
| `TRUNCATE` | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` | n/a | Effectively instant but locks |
| `DROP COLUMN` | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE`, metadata-only | No | Yes — but see below on the hidden dependency |

```sql
-- Lock queue discipline: never let a DDL wait behind long transactions.
SET lock_timeout = '3s';
SET statement_timeout = '30s';
ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN shipped_at timestamptz;
-- If it fails: cancel the ALTER rather than queueing every subsequent
-- query behind it. This one line prevents the classic
-- "one ALTER TABLE queued behind a 30-minute report took the site down".
```

**The four canonical recipes:**

```sql
-- (1) Add a NOT NULL column with a default, without a rewrite or a long lock (PG 11+)
ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN status text NOT NULL DEFAULT 'pending';
-- metadata-only when the default is not volatile. On PG 10 and earlier this rewrote
-- the table; the safe portable form is:
ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN status text;                        -- nullable, instant
ALTER TABLE orders ALTER COLUMN status SET DEFAULT 'pending';     -- instant
-- backfill in batches (see below), then:
ALTER TABLE orders ALTER COLUMN status SET NOT NULL;              -- scans; use the CHECK trick
```

```sql
-- (2) Add NOT NULL to a huge table with a short lock (the CHECK trick)
ALTER TABLE orders ADD CONSTRAINT orders_status_nn
  CHECK (status IS NOT NULL) NOT VALID;            -- brief ACCESS EXCLUSIVE, no scan
-- backfill (batched)
ALTER TABLE orders VALIDATE CONSTRAINT orders_status_nn;  -- SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE, online
-- Now the fast path:
ALTER TABLE orders ALTER COLUMN status SET NOT NULL;     -- PG 12+ skips the scan
ALTER TABLE orders DROP CONSTRAINT orders_status_nn;      -- the NOT NULL now guarantees it
```

```sql
-- (3) Add a foreign key online
ALTER TABLE order_lines ADD CONSTRAINT order_lines_order_fk
  FOREIGN KEY (order_id) REFERENCES orders(id) NOT VALID;
-- backfill / verify existing rows satisfy it (or fix the orphans)
ALTER TABLE order_lines VALIDATE CONSTRAINT order_lines_order_fk;
-- new and updated rows are checked immediately from now on
```

```sql
-- (4) Backfill without a long transaction, without a lock storm, resumably
-- Batch by primary key, in small chunks, in its own transaction.
DO $$
DECLARE
  last_id bigint := 0;
  n int;
BEGIN
  LOOP
    WITH moved AS (
      UPDATE orders SET shipped_at = created_at, updated_at = now()
      WHERE id > last_id AND id <= last_id + 5000 AND shipped_at IS NULL
      RETURNING id
    )
    SELECT max(id), count(*) INTO last_id, n FROM moved;
    COMMIT;                     -- release locks, let vacuum catch up
    EXIT WHEN n = 0;
    PERFORM pg_sleep(0.05);     -- yield, keep WAL and replication lag sane
  END LOOP;
END $$;
```
Rules for backfills: **idempotent** (`WHERE shipped_at IS NULL`), **batched** (500 to 5000 rows), **its own transaction per batch**, **a sleep between batches**, **a progress log**, **a kill switch** (`pg_terminate_backend` the DO block), and **rate-limited** to stay under replication lag and I/O budgets. For very large tables, do the backfill with `pg_repack` or by writing to a new table and swapping (`CREATE TABLE new ...; INSERT; ATTACH;`).

## 6.16.2 The compatibility matrix: what old code needs while new code exists

| Change | Old code needs | New code needs | Safe window |
|---|---|---|---|
| Add column | Column absent is fine (SELECT * breaks — always name columns) | Column present | After deploy 1, before deploy 3 |
| Drop column | Column present | Column absent | Only after old code is fully drained |
| Rename column | Old name | New name | Requires dual-write with both names, or a compatibility view |
| Narrow a type | Wide type | Narrow type | Requires a full table rewrite; do it as add-new/backfill/switch |
| Add `NOT NULL` | Can write NULL? No — old code may omit the column | Must always set it | Only after old code cannot omit it (default!) |
| Add a unique index | Old code can write duplicates | Cannot | Only after old writers are changed |
| Add a FK | Old code can write orphans | Cannot | Only after old writers are changed |
| Split a table | Old shape | New shape | Dual-write + backfill + read switch + a compatibility view for old readers |

**The compatibility view trick**, which is the cleanest way to handle a rename or a table split:

```sql
-- Table is becoming `order_items`, but 200 query references still say `order_lines`.
-- 1. Rename the physical table (instant) -- old code is now broken, so instead:
ALTER TABLE order_lines RENAME TO order_lines_legacy;
CREATE VIEW order_lines AS SELECT id, order_id, sku, qty FROM order_items_v2;
-- 2. New code writes to order_items_v2 through an INSTEAD OF trigger or the app.
-- 3. Migrate readers to order_items_v2 one query at a time.
-- 4. DROP VIEW order_lines; DROP TABLE order_lines_legacy;
-- Note: an updatable simple view works for writes too; a JOIN view needs
-- an INSTEAD OF trigger per operation, which is a maintenance cost.
```

**The dual-write and the ordering problem.** When old code writes column A and new code writes column B (the renamed one), you have two writers and one truth. Options:

| Strategy | How | Risk |
|---|---|---|
| Dual-write in the application (new code writes both) | Simple | Old code (still running) writes only the old column; need a DB trigger to mirror, or accept a window of divergence |
| DB trigger to mirror old → new | `AFTER INSERT OR UPDATE` writing the new column when the old is set | Two writers to the same row = lock contention and lost updates if done carelessly |
| Keep both columns and a **view** that coalesces | `COALESCE(new_col, old_col)` | Simplest and safest; clean up later |
| Make the new column the source of truth and backfill, then flip reads | Standard | Needs old code to tolerate a NULL in the new column |

The honest ordering rule: **never make the new column required before old code is gone; never drop the old column before the new column is populated and verified.**

## 6.16.3 Index lifecycle: create, verify, retire

```sql
-- Create without blocking writes
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY idx_orders_tenant_created
  ON orders (tenant_id, created_at DESC);
-- If it fails (e.g. a duplicate value), you are left with an INVALID index
-- that is maintained but never used. Check and clean up:
SELECT c.relname, i.indisvalid, i.indisready
FROM pg_index i JOIN pg_class c ON c.oid = i.indexrelid
WHERE NOT i.indisvalid;

-- Verify the planner actually uses it before you retire the old one
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) SELECT ... WHERE tenant_id = 7 ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 50;
-- watch pg_stat_user_indexes: idx_scan must be > 0 over a representative window

-- Retire: drop (fast), but only after the old index shows idx_scan = 0
DROP INDEX CONCURRENTLY idx_orders_old;   -- Postgres supports CONCURRENTLY for DROP INDEX
```
The trap: `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` runs two passes and takes longer than `CREATE INDEX`; it cannot run inside a transaction; and it can leave an invalid index behind. Also, an index that is `indisvalid` but for a query whose plan was cached with a different plan can show `idx_scan = 0` and still be needed — so retire based on a full traffic cycle (a week, covering weekly jobs), not an hour.

## 6.16.4 Migration safety as a discipline

**Tooling and gates:**
- **Migrations are code.** They live in version control, are reviewed, and are applied by CI (`prisma migrate deploy`, Flyway, Liquibase, `yoyo`, or a `dbmate`-style runner). Nobody runs DDL from a laptop.
- **Separate "apply" from "verify".** Every migration has a post-condition: `SELECT count(*) FROM information_schema.columns WHERE ...`, a data invariant (row counts, checksum of a sample), and an assertion that reads and writes still work. Emit these as metrics.
- **One migration per deploy, forward-only.** No down migrations that drop data. The rollback for a bad migration is *code* rollback (deploy the old code), and the schema change is cleaned up in a later forward migration. This is the only rollback model that works with a distributed fleet.
- **Advisory lock on migrations.** Two app instances starting simultaneously must not both run migrations. Postgres: `SELECT pg_advisory_lock(hashtext('migrations'));` in the runner. Flyway/Liquibase have this built in.
- **Feature flags.** The migration and the feature rollout are independent. `ALTER TABLE` succeeds; the new code path is behind a flag; the flag ramps 1% → 10% → 100% with error-rate and latency gates. This is the difference between a migration and an outage.
- **Canary the schema.** For the riskiest changes, apply the migration to one replica/read-replica first, or to a shadow environment restored from a production backup with production-like data volume, and run the query suite against it.

**The kill switch and the safety rails:**
```sql
-- Every long-running migration operation should be interruptible:
SET lock_timeout = '3s';
SET statement_timeout = 0;          -- long but interruptible via statement cancel
SET idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = '60s';
-- and monitored:
SELECT pid, now() - query_start AS dur, left(query, 120)
FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE state = 'active' AND query LIKE '%ALTER%';
```

**Rollback semantics, stated honestly:**
| Failure point | What you do |
|---|---|
| Migration fails mid-way (transactional DDL on Postgres) | Transaction rolls back; nothing changed; fix and re-run |
| Migration applies but old code breaks (e.g. you dropped a column too early) | Deploy the previous application version; the schema is additive so it still works; then forward-fix |
| Migration applies, new code has a bug | Roll back the code; the schema change stays; it is additive |
| Data backfill wrong (wrong values written) | Forward migration to correct, or restore from backup (expensive) — which is why backfills are staged, verified, and shadow-written first |
| Migration succeeded but is performance-degrading (a new index doubling write cost) | Drop the index `CONCURRENTLY`; no code change needed |

**A model answer for "how do you do a zero-downtime migration":** "Context: `orders.tenant_id` is currently nullable because the earliest 2% of rows predate multi-tenancy; we now need `NOT NULL` and a foreign key to `tenants`, on a table of 800M rows receiving 3k writes/s. Plan: (1) Deploy 1, expand: add the FK as `NOT VALID` (brief lock, no scan), and set a default for `tenant_id` so new rows are always populated. (2) Backfill the 16M nulls in 5000-row batches with a sleep, tracking progress in a metrics table and watching replication lag, with the ability to `pg_cancel_backend` if lag exceeds a threshold. (3) Validate the constraint with `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` (online, `SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE`). (4) Deploy 2: `SET NOT NULL` — which in PG 12+ is instant because the validated `CHECK`/`NOT NULL` is already there; we do it via the `CHECK ... NOT VALID` → validate → `SET NOT NULL` → drop `CHECK` sequence. (5) Deploy 3, contract: nothing to drop here, but the old single-tenant code paths go away behind a feature flag first. Every step has `lock_timeout = '3s'` so nothing queues behind long transactions, and the whole sequence is reversible by rolling back code, never by a down migration. Trade-off: this is 3 deploys over ~1 week rather than a 4-minute change; the alternative is a maintenance window, which for a 3k writes/s system is 8 figures of deferred revenue per hour."

**Common mistakes**
1. Running DDL without `lock_timeout`, so a `CREATE INDEX` or `ALTER TABLE` queues behind a long report and blocks *every* subsequent query on the table. The queue is the outage.
2. Adding a `NOT NULL` column with a volatile default on PG 10 or earlier and discovering it rewrites 800M rows under an `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` lock.
3. `CREATE INDEX` (not `CONCURRENTLY`) on a hot table during peak traffic.
4. Renaming a column in one deploy, breaking 200 queries at once.
5. Deploying code that reads the new column in the same deploy that creates it, then rolling the code back: now the code expects a column that the rolled-back migration would have... (this is fine if migrations are forward-only, but breaks if you also roll back the migration).
6. Running the migration from a developer's laptop against production.
7. A backfill in one big transaction, holding locks and bloating the table and blocking vacuum for an hour.
8. Forgetting that the `ORDER BY`/index change requires a plan-cache and prepared-statement refresh in some drivers.
9. Two instances running the migration concurrently (no advisory lock).
10. Dropping the old column in the same deploy as adding the new one because "the deploy is atomic" — the deploy is atomic per instance, and instances roll at different times, so for the duration of the rollout you have a mixed fleet. This is the mistake the whole section is about.

**Interviewer probes**
- "Your `ALTER TABLE` is queued behind a long-running query and now everything is stuck. What do you do?" → `SELECT pg_cancel_backend(pid)` / `pg_terminate_backend(pid)` on the `ALTER` itself; if the queue is already built, cancelling the head of the queue is the only thing that unblocks the chain; then set `lock_timeout` so it never happens again, and fix the long query.
- "How do you add `NOT NULL` to a 500M-row table?" → `CHECK (col IS NOT NULL) NOT VALID` → backfill → `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` → `SET NOT NULL` (fast path) → `DROP CONSTRAINT`.
- "Your `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` failed. What is the state?" → An `INVALID` index that is still maintained on write but never used by the planner; check `pg_index.indisvalid`, drop it, fix the cause (duplicate values, a unique constraint violation), re-run.
- "How do you rename a column with zero downtime?" → Never. Add the new column, dual-write, backfill, switch reads, deploy old code drain, drop the old column. Three deploys.
- "Why not write down migrations?" → Because the fleet is mixed-version during a rollout and after a code rollback: a down migration would drop data that the restored code expects. Code rollback + forward-only schema is the only safe combination.

---

# 6.17 Caching Layers (canonical)

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition.** A cache is a store that holds a copy of data computed elsewhere, kept for a bounded time, to serve reads faster and to protect the system of record. The canonical section in this phase because caching is where SQL knowledge becomes systems knowledge: it is the layer where latency budgets, correctness, and failure modes all collide, and where the most confident wrong answers live ("we cache with a 5-minute TTL" as if TTL were a policy).

**The frame to open with.** Caching is not primarily a performance technique; it is a **staleness contract**. Every cached read answers the question "how wrong is it acceptable to be, for how long, and who decides?" If you cannot answer that for a given cache entry, you do not have a cache, you have a bug with a low reproduction rate.

---

## 6.17.1 Why caching is a correctness problem, not a performance trick

**Definition.** A cache entry is a *correctness liability* when a stale read can authorize an action, when it is irreversible once acted upon, or when the invalidation path is not guaranteed to run. Performance caching (a rendered product page) is an optimization. Correctness caching (an entitlement check) is a distributed systems problem, because it introduces a second source of truth whose consistency you must now reason about on every write path.

**The three tests for "is this entry a correctness liability?":**

1. **Authorization test.** Can a stale value let someone do something they should not? (feature flags for paid features, permission checks, entitlement, fraud/risk scores, geo-restrictions, pricing used at checkout, legal consent.)
2. **Irreversibility test.** Once the stale value has been used, can you take it back? (an emailed invoice, a shipped label, a charged card, a published post, an exported CSV, a sent SMS.)
3. **Propagation test.** Does the write path guarantee the invalidation happens, atomically, and exactly once? (An `UPDATE` in one service, an `UPDATE` in a batch job, a direct `psql` edit, and a rollback all produce different invalidation outcomes.)

If (1) or (2) is true, a plain TTL cache is the wrong structure. Use one of:
- **Event-driven invalidation** (outbox + consumer, or Pub/Sub) *in addition to* a TTL as a safety net.
- **Versioned keys**: the cache key includes a version (e.g. `entitlements:{user_id}:{plan_version}`), and a write creates a new version rather than overwriting. Old versions expire naturally; readers see either the old or the new value, never a torn one.
- **Read-through from the authority with a hard invariant**: never cache at all; instead, make the read fast (index, replica) and pay the consistency.
- **Push-on-write (write-through)**: the write path updates the cache inside the same transaction boundary as best you can, with the known caveat in 6.17.2.

**The second-order costs nobody lists:**
- **Cache-key cardinality.** Every distinct key is memory, and memory is finite. An unbounded cache is a memory leak with a TTL-shaped disguise; it evicts the useful entries (LRU evicts the coldest, which are often exactly the working set you need when the cache is too small).
- **Cache keys are an API.** `user:{id}:profile` is depended upon by a service, a script, and a dashboard. Renaming it is a migration. Version keys: `user:{id}:profile:v2`.
- **A cache turns a "no data" bug into an intermittent bug.** Everything that is hard to debug about distributed systems gets *harder* when a cache is in the middle, because the same request returns different answers.
- **Cache availability is now part of your availability.** If the cache is a hard dependency (your code errors when Redis is down), you have made Redis a Tier-1 dependency with sub-millisecond SLOs. If it is soft (fail to the database), you have chosen to let the database take the load — which is only safe if you also built the stampede protections in 6.17.4 and can shed load.
- **The cost of a cache hit is not zero.** A Redis GET is 0.2ms in-region, 2ms cross-region, 30ms cross-region on another continent. A cache hit that crosses a continent is slower than the query it replaced.

**Interviewer framing that lands:** "I treat a cache as a documented staleness contract: for each entry I know the maximum staleness, the invalidation trigger, the failure behaviour when the cache is down, and the blast radius if the invalidation is missed. If I cannot fill in those four fields, I do not add the cache."

---

## 6.17.2 Cache-aside, write-through, write-back

**Definition.** The three write/read patterns for keeping a cache and a store of record in sync.

### Cache-aside (lazy loading)

```sql
-- Read path (the only pattern you need 90% of the time)
SELECT * FROM users WHERE id = $1;
```

```ts
async function getUser(id: string): Promise<User> {
  const key = `user:v1:${id}`;
  const hit = await redis.get(key);
  if (hit !== null) return JSON.parse(hit) as User;

  // Single-flight: collapse concurrent misses for the same key into one DB read.
  return singleFlight(key, async () => {
    const user = await db.query('SELECT * FROM users WHERE id = $1', [id]);
    if (!user) {
      // Negative caching: remember the miss briefly so a hot nonexistent id
      // (an enumeration attack, a broken link) does not hammer the DB.
      await redis.set(`user:v1:${id}:missing`, '1', { EX: 30 });
      throw new NotFound();
    }
    await redis.set(key, JSON.stringify(user), { EX: jitter(300, 360) });
    return user;
  });
}
```

```ts
// Write path: delete, do not update. Invalidation is idempotent and cheaper to reason about.
async function updateUser(id: string, patch: Partial<User>) {
  const user = await db.query(
    'UPDATE users SET name = $2, plan = $3, updated_at = now() WHERE id = $1 RETURNING *',
    [id, patch.name, patch.plan],
  );
  await redis.del(`user:v1:${id}`);
  // Optionally warm it, but only if you accept the read cost synchronously.
  return user.rows[0];
}
```

| Property | Cache-aside |
|---|---|
| Who writes the cache | The reader (on a miss) |
| Staleness window | From the store write until the invalidation; with a TTL, bounded by TTL |
| Miss cost | One store read + one cache write (2x latency on the first request after expiry) |
| Failure of the cache | Every read goes to the store; no data loss; the store must survive the extra load |
| Complexity | Lowest; invalidation is a single `DEL`; the cache is disposable and rebuildable |
| Main risk | Stampede on expiry (6.17.4) and stale reads if `DEL` is lost |
| When the write fails to invalidate | Silent staleness until TTL. Mitigate with Pub/Sub invalidation on top (an optimization, not a guarantee) |

**The lost-invalidation problem, stated precisely:** `UPDATE users SET plan='pro'` commits; then the process crashes before `redis.del`. The cache now serves `plan='free'` for up to the TTL. This is why:
- Put the `DEL` *before* the DB write (fail-safe direction: worst case, an extra miss, not stale data) — but that opens a race where a concurrent read repopulates the stale value *after* your `DEL` and *before* your commit. The correct order is: `DEL` → `UPDATE` → (optionally `DEL` again), or better, use an outbox.
- Use a **transactional outbox** so the invalidation is emitted in the same transaction as the write and delivered at-least-once by a relay. That converts a lost invalidation into a delayed one, bounded by relay lag, and the TTL is the backstop.

### Write-through

```ts
// Every write updates the store and the cache, together.
async function setPrice(productId: string, priceCents: number) {
  const ok = await redis.set(`price:v1:${productId}`, JSON.stringify({ priceCents }), { EX: 600 });
  if (!ok) throw new CacheWriteFailed();     // decide: fail the write, or continue?
  return db.query('UPDATE products SET price_cents = $2 WHERE id = $1', [productId, priceCents]);
}
```

| Property | Write-through |
|---|---|
| Who writes the cache | The writer, on every write |
| Staleness | Near-zero on the write path; a read never triggers a store read |
| Read cost | Always a cache hit (or a miss if the cache was lost, e.g. a Redis restart) |
| **The dual write** | Two writes to two systems with no shared transaction. Either can succeed and the other fail: the cache says new, the store says old (or the reverse) |
| Failure mode | A cache write failure forces a policy decision: fail the request (availability suffers, correctness guaranteed) or proceed (correctness suffers) |
| Mitigations | Write cache first then store (fail the request if the cache write fails), store first then cache (a window of staleness), or outbox + async cache update (a window of staleness, no request-path failure) |
| When the cache is evicted | A read returns stale-or-missing with no way to know; with write-through, a cache loss means *every* read is a miss, so you need a bulk warm on recovery |
| Use when | The read is far hotter than the write (ratios of 100:1 or more), the value is cheap to recompute, and a brief staleness window is acceptable |

**The honest verdict on write-through:** it does not eliminate staleness, it relocates it to the write path and adds a new failure mode (a failed cache write on a successful store write). Cache-aside with a TTL *and* a correct invalidation path is more robust and simpler. Write-through earns its place when read latency is the whole point (e.g. a pricing lookup on a hot checkout path) and the read:write ratio is extreme.

### Write-back (write-behind)

```ts
// The cache is the write buffer; the store is updated asynchronously in batches.
async function incrementViewCount(articleId: string) {
  await redis.hincrby('views', articleId, 1);
  // A flusher every N seconds writes dirty fields back; a crash loses N seconds.
}
```

| Property | Write-back |
|---|---|
| Who writes the store | A background flusher |
| Write latency | Very low (only the cache write) |
| Durability | **The cache is now the only copy until flushed.** A Redis restart loses unflushed writes. AOF `everysec` gives you ~1 second |
| Correctness | Requires a dirty-set (a Redis set of pending keys, or a stream) and idempotent, order-independent flushes |
| Good for | Counters, view counts, "last seen" timestamps, analytics aggregates, sensor readings — where a small loss is acceptable and the value is explicitly approximate |
| Bad for | Anything financial, anything with an SLA, anything a user would notice missing |

The failure mode that ends careers: write-back on a value that turns out to be authoritative later. "The view counter is eventually consistent" is fine; "the payment status is eventually consistent" is an incident. Rule: **write-back only for values whose spec already says they are approximate.**

### A fourth pattern worth naming: write-around

On a write to a key that is not currently cached, do *not* insert it into the cache (just invalidate/delete). This prevents one-shot reads (a scan of 10,000 IDs) from evicting the entire hot working set. Correct for high-cardinality, low-reuse reads.

### Comparison table

| Dimension | Cache-aside | Write-through | Write-back | Write-around |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Store is authoritative | Yes | Yes | **No** (temporarily) | Yes |
| Read path store access | On miss | Never (until eviction) | Never | On miss |
| Staleness on write | Bounded by invalidation (TTL as backstop) | Minimal | Bounded by flush interval | n/a |
| Invalidation required | **Yes** | No | No | n/a |
| Failure of cache | Reads hit store; load spike | All reads miss | **Data loss for unflushed writes** | Reads hit store |
| Complexity | Low | Medium | High (dirty set, flusher, idempotency) | Low |
| Best fit | Default for most reads | Very hot reads, rare writes | Approximate counters/aggregates | High-cardinality one-shot reads |

---

## 6.17.3 TTL strategy, eviction policies, and negative caching

**Definition.** **TTL** is the maximum lifetime of an entry. **Eviction** is the policy by which a memory-constrained store removes entries before the TTL expires. **Negative caching** is storing the *absence* of a key so that repeated misses do not reach the store.

### TTL as a function of the staleness contract

There is no default TTL. There is a derivation:

```
TTL = staleness_budget
    + time_to_invalidate (the relay/consume latency, p99)
    + safety_margin (say 25%)
```

And separately, for a read-heavy cache you often want:

```
TTL = 0.25 * (time it takes the underlying data to change often enough that
              a stale read is noticeable)
```

The practical rules:
- **Long TTL** (hours to days): data that rarely changes and where a stale read is harmless (country lists, product categories, feature flag defaults, historical reference data). Invalidate by event on top.
- **Medium TTL** (minutes): derived values (aggregates, rankings, recommendations), read-heavy pages.
- **Short TTL** (seconds): anything where the user can act on the value (prices, availability, stock counts) — but note a short TTL does *not* make a correctness liability safe, it only bounds the damage.
- **No TTL, event-invalidated**: correctness-critical values where the invalidation is reliable and the TTL is only a safety net. Choose a long TTL (hours) so a missed invalidation self-heals within a day rather than poisoning for a week.
- **Negative TTL**: usually much shorter than the positive TTL (5 to 30 seconds), because a negative entry suppresses real data appearing; but it must be long enough to stop a hot-key attack. A negative TTL of 30s on a 404 means a newly created resource 404s for up to 30 seconds. Use a *short* negative TTL (5 to 10s) for resources that can appear, and a *long* one (60s+) for resources that are genuinely nonexistent (an unknown tenant id).

### Jitter: the single highest-value line of code in this section

```ts
export function jitter(baseSeconds: number, spread = 0.2): number {
  const delta = baseSeconds * spread * (Math.random() * 2 - 1);   // ±20%
  return Math.max(1, Math.round(baseSeconds + delta));
}
```

Fixed TTLs on keys populated together expire together. At scale this creates a periodic cliff: at T+300s, 40% of requests miss simultaneously, all hit the database, the database saturates, latency explodes, and cache hit rate collapses. With ±20% jitter, the misses spread out and peak miss rate drops by roughly an order of magnitude. Do it for *every* TTL you set, including the negative TTL and the lock TTL.

### Eviction policies (Redis `maxmemory-policy`)

| Policy | Evicts | Use for | Failure mode |
|---|---|---|---|
| `noeviction` | Nothing; writes fail with OOM | Rate limiters, locks, sessions, idempotency keys, anything authoritative-ish | Write errors when full — **which is the correct failure**: loud, not silent |
| `allkeys-lru` | Approximate LRU across all keys | A pure cache instance | Mixed data classes: hot session keys can be evicted by cache churn |
| `allkeys-lfu` | Approximate LFU (frequency-based, with a decay) | A pure cache with skewed access | Same, but adapts better to changing hotness |
| `allkeys-random` | Random | Benchmarks, never production | — |
| `volatile-lru` / `volatile-lfu` | Only keys *with* a TTL, LRU/LFU | Instances where the TTL-bearing keys are the disposable ones | **Dangerous**: if a key has no TTL it is *never* evicted, and if all your important keys have no TTL, memory fills and Redis starts failing |
| `volatile-ttl` | Keys with the nearest TTL | Very specific cases | Same danger |
| `allkeys-lru` on a mixed instance | — | **Avoid**: sessions, locks, and rate-limit counters live in an evictable keyspace and get silently dropped |

**The rule that matters: one Redis instance, one data class.** Caches on `allkeys-lru` instances; sessions on `allkeys-lru` (they are rebuildable — losing one logs a user out, acceptable) *or* on a dedicated instance with a longer TTL; locks, rate limits, idempotency keys, and counters on a `noeviction` instance. Mixing them means either the cache evicts your lock (two clients hold the same "lock") or the limiter's memory growth forces you into `volatile-*` and the cache starts failing writes.

Redis's LRU/LFU are **approximate** — a random sample of keys is used to build the candidate set, which is why they are cheap. Also note `maxmemory` counts memory across the instance, so a Redis with `maxmemory 4gb` on a 4 GB instance will be OOM-killed by the kernel before Redis starts evicting. Always leave headroom (`maxmemory` ≈ 60 to 75% of the instance RAM).

### Negative caching, done properly

```ts
// Sentinel value approach: one key, explicit negative marker
const MISS = '\u0000MISS';
async function getProfile(id: string) {
  const key = `profile:v1:${id}`;
  const raw = await redis.get(key);
  if (raw === null) return loadProfile(id);
  if (raw === MISS) throw new NotFound();
  return JSON.parse(raw);
}

async function loadProfile(id: string) {
  return singleFlight(key(id), async () => {
    // Re-check inside the flight: another request may have just loaded it.
    const raw = await redis.get(key(id));
    if (raw !== null) return raw === MISS ? Promise.reject(new NotFound()) : JSON.parse(raw);
    const row = await db.query('SELECT * FROM profiles WHERE id = $1', [id]);
    if (!row) {
      await redis.set(key(id), MISS, { EX: 5 });       // short: resources appear
      throw new NotFound();
    }
    await redis.set(key(id), JSON.stringify(row), { EX: jitter(3600, 0.2) });
    return row;
  });
}
```

Negative caching traps:
- **Do not confuse "cached negative" with "not found forever".** A 30s negative TTL on a just-created user is a visible bug.
- **Do not let an attacker turn your DB into a negative-cache bypass.** If negative entries are stored under a *different* key than positives and you only single-flight positives, an enumeration attack hits the DB every time. Store the sentinel in the same key.
- **Guard memory:** an attacker enumerating 1,000,000 IDs creates 1,000,000 negative entries. Rate-limit the enumeration path (6.17.10) and/or use a probabilistic structure (a Bloom filter) for "definitely does not exist" checks on a large keyspace.
- **`EXISTS` vs a sentinel:** `EXISTS` costs a round trip and cannot distinguish "absent" from "cached negative"; use one key with a sentinel value if you need to cache negatives at all.

---

## 6.17.4 Cache stampede, dog-pile, and thundering herd protection

**Definition.** A **cache stampede** (or **dog-pile**) is a burst of concurrent requests for the same expired key, each of which misses and each of which issues its own expensive store read. A **thundering herd** is the same idea at scale: many keys expiring simultaneously, or a cache restart causing every key to miss at once.

**Mechanics.** Consider a key with 10,000 req/s and a 300s TTL, computed by a 50ms query. One expiry means 10,000 requests each start their own 50ms query within the same few milliseconds. The database sees a 10,000× spike in a query that normally runs 200 times/s. It saturates, latency for *all* queries goes up, and now the requests that would have hit *other* keys also time out — the failure spreads. The cache is what caused the outage it was added to prevent.

**Four defences, in order of how you should actually use them:**

**1. Single-flight (in-process request coalescing).** Collapse concurrent misses for the same key into one store read. This is the cheapest and most effective defence and it needs no Redis.

```ts
class SingleFlight<K, V> {
  private inflight = new Map<K, Promise<V>>();

  async do(key: K, fn: () => Promise<V>): Promise<V> {
    const existing = this.inflight.get(key);
    if (existing) return existing;
    const p = (async () => fn())().finally(() => this.inflight.delete(key));
    this.inflight.set(key, p);
    return p;
  }
}

const flight = new SingleFlight<string, User>();
export const getUser = (id: string) => flight.do(`user:v1:${id}`, () => loadFromDb(id));
```

Note the **limitation to state in an interview**: this works per process. With 50 pods, 50 concurrent misses still happen — but 50 identical queries is a rounding error compared to 10,000. Combine with a distributed lock if the query is genuinely expensive, or with stale-while-revalidate so nobody blocks.

**2. Stale-while-revalidate (SWR).** Serve the stale value while one background process refreshes it. No reader ever blocks on a miss after the first fill.

```ts
// Two keys per logical entry: the value (with a long soft TTL) and the freshness marker.
async function getSWR<T>(key: string, compute: () => Promise<T>): Promise<T | null> {
  const raw = await redis.get(key);
  if (raw !== null) {
    const entry = JSON.parse(raw) as { v: T; freshUntil: number; staleUntil: number };
    if (Date.now() < entry.freshUntil) return entry.v;                 // fresh
    if (Date.now() < entry.staleUntil && !refreshInProgress.has(key)) {
      void refresh(key, compute);                                      // one background refresh
      return entry.v;                                                  // serve stale
    }
  }
  // Cold, or beyond staleUntil: block (but single-flight so only one blocks)
  return singleFlight(key, async () => {
    const fresh = await computeAndStore(key, compute);
    return fresh;
  });
}

async function refresh(key: string, compute: () => Promise<T>) {
  if (refreshInProgress.has(key)) return;
  refreshInProgress.add(key);
  try {
    await computeAndStore(key, compute);
  } catch (err) {
    logger.error({ err, key }, 'SWR refresh failed; extending stale window');
    // Extend staleUntil rather than dropping the entry: serving stale beats serving 500.
    await redis.hset(`${key}:meta`, 'staleUntil', String(Date.now() + STALE_GRACE));
  } finally {
    refreshInProgress.delete(key);
  }
}
```

SWR introduces **stale serving as a feature**, which is why it needs a stated budget: "we serve up to 5 minutes stale on a product page and never more than 30 minutes stale even if the refresh keeps failing." That "never more than" clause is the difference between a documented contract and hope.

**3. Distributed lock around the recompute.** Only one process across the fleet refreshes a key.

```ts
async function withRefreshLock<T>(key: string, fn: () => Promise<T>): Promise<T | null> {
  const token = crypto.randomUUID();
  const lockKey = `lock:refresh:${key}`;
  const ok = await redis.set(lockKey, token, { NX: true, PX: 5000 });
  if (!ok) {
    // Someone else is refreshing. Either wait briefly, or return stale.
    return readStale(key);
  }
  try {
    const fresh = await fn();
    await redis.set(key, encode(fresh), { EX: 60 });
    return fresh;
  } finally {
    // Atomic compare-and-delete; never delete someone else's lock.
    await redis.eval(RELEASE_LUA, { keys: [lockKey], arguments: [token] });
  }
}
const RELEASE_LUA = `
  if redis.call("GET", KEYS[1]) == ARGV[1] then
    return redis.call("DEL", KEYS[1])
  else
    return 0
  end`;
```

Lock TTL must exceed the compute time with margin, and if the compute can exceed the TTL you must renew (6.17.5). A lock whose TTL is shorter than the work is not a lock; it is a race with extra steps.

**4. Probabilistic early recompute (XFetch).** Recompute before the TTL expires, at a probability that rises as the TTL approaches, so refreshes are spread evenly instead of clustered.

```ts
// XFetch: recompute with probability p = delta * log(t)/-log(epsilon),
// where delta = time since last recompute, t = TTL, epsilon = staleness ratio.
function shouldRecompute(deltaMs: number, ttlMs: number, epsilon = 0.05): boolean {
  const delta = -ttlMs * Math.log(epsilon);          // time at which recompute probability = 1
  const p = Math.min(1, (deltaMs / -Math.log(epsilon)) / ttlMs * (Math.log(1 / epsilon)) / -Math.log(1 / epsilon));
  return Math.random() < p;
}
```
Simpler and nearly as effective: **recompute when the remaining TTL is below a random threshold drawn from the TTL's own distribution** (i.e. jitter, applied to *reads* rather than to writes). And the cheapest of all: **a background refresher** (`keyspace notifications` on expiry, or a scheduled job that warms known hot keys) so the TTL is never actually reached.

**Thundering herd beyond a single key:**
- **Cache restart / flush.** Every key misses at once. Mitigation: warm the top-N keys on startup or on a schedule before allowing traffic (`readiness` gate), or set a large `min-cacheable-size` and rely on a "serve stale from the previous process" local L1 (which also makes Redis optional for that tier).
- **Many keys expiring together.** Fixed TTL on a whole query result set (e.g. every tenant's dashboard cached at the same time because you warmed them in a loop) → always jitter.
- **Stampede across regions.** Use per-region locking/refresh, not one global lock, otherwise a global lock is itself a single point of failure during a Redis blip.

**Monitoring (this is what makes it production-grade):**
```
cache_hit_ratio{miss_type="positive"}      # watch for step changes
cache_refresh_inflight                     # if this is nonzero for minutes, refreshes are stuck
cache_refresh_duration_seconds             # must be << lock TTL
cache_stale_served_total                   # your staleness budget, in practice
db_queries_per_second{origin="cache-fill"} # the load the cache is adding; must be << normal load
lock_wait_seconds{op="cache-refresh"}      # how many requests are waiting
```

**Common mistakes**
1. Single-flight only, with a large fleet and an expensive compute: 50 concurrent DB queries is still a spike.
2. A refresh lock TTL shorter than the compute time.
3. `DEL` without checking the token, so one process releases another's lock.
4. SWR with no `staleUntil` ceiling, so a persistently failing compute serves day-old data forever.
5. Jitter only on the *value* TTL and not on the negative TTL or the lock TTL.
6. A cache that is a hard dependency with no fail-open path, so a Redis blip is a total outage.
7. No metric distinguishing "cache miss" from "cache fill for a key that never existed".
8. Rate-limiting key generation by user-supplied input without a canonicalization step, so `?page=1&page=01` are two keys.
9. Warming the cache in a loop with a fixed TTL on every key, creating the herd you were avoiding.
10. Treating "hit ratio" as the goal. A 99% hit ratio on a cache that serves 1% of traffic is worth less than an 80% hit ratio on the 99% hot path. Measure hit ratio per key class.

**Interviewer probes**
- "A hot key expires and the DB falls over. Walk me through the fix." → Immediate: enable SWR or a refresh lock so no reader blocks; single-flight so only one process recomputes; TTL jitter so it does not recur; add hit-ratio and fill-QPS metrics; then find why one key had 10k req/s (add an L1 local cache or precompute it).
- "Why is single-flight not enough?" → It is per process; with N pods you get N misses, and it does nothing for the herd across *different* keys or after a cache restart.
- "What is XFetch and why does it help?" → Recompute before expiry with probability rising as staleness approaches, spreading refresh load continuously instead of in a cliff at the TTL boundary.

---

## 6.17.5 Distributed locks: correctness, failure modes, and fencing tokens

**Definition.** A distributed lock is a mechanism that ensures at most one of N processes performs a critical section at a time, across process boundaries. It is the most over-used and most frequently *wrongly implemented* piece of infrastructure in backend systems.

**Start with the honest question: do you need one?** The strong answers are almost always better:
- **A unique constraint in the database** is a lock, and a perfect one (it is atomic, transactional, and deadlock-free). "Only one active subscription per user" is a partial unique index, not a Redis lock.
- **An atomic conditional update** is a lock: `UPDATE jobs SET status='running', locked_by=$1 WHERE id=$2 AND status='pending' RETURNING id` — one of those succeeds and the other gets zero rows.
- **A serializable transaction with `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE`** or advisory locks (6.6, 6.9) is a lock with real guarantees, scoped to the transaction, auto-released on crash.
- **Idempotency keys** remove the need for mutual exclusion in the classic "process the payment exactly once" case.
- **A queue with `SKIP LOCKED`** is a lock-free work distribution mechanism.

Only reach for a Redis lock when the critical section is *outside* the database, spans multiple systems, or must be held across a long time period (a leader election, a nightly batch, a rate-limit-of-one-concurrent-cron).

### The implementation, done correctly

```ts
const ACQUIRE = `
  -- Acquire: set only if absent, with a lease
  if redis.call("SET", KEYS[1], ARGV[1], "NX", "PX", ARGV[2]) then
    return 1
  else
    return 0
  end`;

async function acquire(lockKey: string, ttlMs: number): Promise<string | null> {
  const token = crypto.randomUUID();     // 1. a unique token per acquisition
  const ok = await redis.eval(ACQUIRE, { keys: [lockKey], arguments: [token, String(ttlMs)] });
  return ok === 1 ? token : null;
}

const RELEASE = `
  -- Release: only if we still own it (compare-and-delete)
  if redis.call("GET", KEYS[1]) == ARGV[1] then
    return redis.call("DEL", KEYS[1])
  else
    return 0
  end`;

async function release(lockKey: string, token: string) {
  return redis.eval(RELEASE, { keys: [lockKey], arguments: [token] });
}

async function withLock<T>(lockKey: string, ttlMs: number, fn: () => Promise<T>): Promise<T> {
  const deadline = Date.now() + ttlMs;
  let token = await acquire(lockKey, ttlMs);
  while (token === null) {
    if (Date.now() > deadline) throw new LockTimeout(lockKey);
    await sleep(50 + Math.random() * 100);   // jittered backoff; not a hot spin
    token = await acquire(lockKey, ttlMs);
  }
  try {
    return await fn();
  } finally {
    await release(lockKey, token);           // 2. always, including on throw
  }
}
```

### The failure modes, named precisely

| Failure mode | What happens | Mitigation |
|---|---|---|
| **No lease (TTL)** | A crashed holder leaves the lock forever; the system wedges | Always set a TTL. There is no "no TTL" lock in production |
| **Lease expires while the holder is still working** | A second process acquires the lock; both are in the critical section. This is the fundamental problem | **Fencing tokens** (below). A lease without a fence is only a mutual-exclusion *hint* |
| **Process pause (GC, VM stop, `SIGSTOP`, container throttling, a slow disk)** between acquire and act | The lease expires, another process takes the lock, the paused process resumes and acts with stale authority | Fencing tokens. Note that Redlock's published criticism is exactly this scenario |
| **Clock skew** | A client-side TTL computation thinks the lease is longer than it is | Always let the *store* own the TTL (`PX`), never compute expiry client-side |
| **Release without a token check** | Process A's slow release deletes process B's lock | Compare-and-delete in one Lua script (above) |
| **Non-atomic acquire** | `SETNX` then `SET`/`EXPIRE` in two round trips: a crash between them leaves a lock with no TTL | One Lua script or `SET key val NX PX ttl` |
| **Lock leak via pooling** | A session-level advisory lock is returned to a pool and never released; the next borrower inherits it (6.6.3) | Use transaction-scoped advisory locks (`pg_advisory_xact_lock`), which release at commit/rollback |
| **Redis is a single point of failure** | Lock service down → either everything blocks or everything proceeds | Choose the failure behaviour deliberately; for correctness locks, fail *closed*; consider a consensus store (etcd/ZooKeeper) for leader election |
| **Clock-bound waits** | Everyone retries at exactly the same moment (a "thundering herd" of lock waiters) | Jittered backoff, as above |
| **Nested locks / lock ordering** | Two code paths acquire A then B, and B then A: deadlock | Global lock ordering, or avoid nesting |
| **Wrong abstraction: "distributed mutex" for a leader election** | Split brain with two leaders | Use a consensus store with a real lease/membership protocol |

### Fencing tokens: the actual solution

**Definition.** A fencing token is a monotonically increasing number issued by the lock service on each acquisition. The *resource* (the thing being protected) records the highest token it has seen and **rejects any operation with a lower token**. This converts "am I still the lock holder?" (which cannot be answered reliably) into "is my token newer than the last one you accepted?" (which is checkable, atomically, at the resource, with no clock and no network).

```sql
-- The resource-side check: a fence column on the protected row.
CREATE TABLE report_runs (
  id           bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  status       text NOT NULL DEFAULT 'pending',
  fence        bigint NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  updated_at   timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
);

-- Every write by the lock holder carries its token.
UPDATE report_runs
SET status = 'complete', fence = $2, updated_at = now()
WHERE id = $1 AND fence <= $2;      -- reject stale holders
```

```ts
// The token must be monotonic across acquisitions. A Redis INCR on a
// dedicated key is monotonic for a single Redis master; for a Redis Cluster
// you need hash tags so the counter lives in one slot.
const token = await redis.incr(`fence:report_runs`);      // monotonic, cheap
await db.query(
  `UPDATE report_runs SET status = $2, fence = $3, updated_at = now()
   WHERE id = $1 AND fence <= $3`,
  [runId, 'running', token],
);
```

A worked example of why fencing is required, and it is worth narrating in an interview:

```
t=0    Pod A acquires the lock with token 41, TTL 30s. Starts a 60s report.
t=5    Pod A stalls (a 55s GC pause / a container CPU throttle / a disk stall).
t=30   Lease expires. Redis has no idea. Pod A still believes it holds the lock.
t=31   Pod B acquires the lock with token 42, TTL 30s. Starts the report.
t=32   Pod B writes status='running', fence=42. Accepted.
t=60   Pod A un-stalls. It still believes it holds the lock (its token 41 never
       expired in its own mind). It writes status='complete' with fence=41.
       The resource checks 41 <= 42 -> FALSE -> REJECTED.
Without the fence, Pod A's write lands and the report is marked complete with
half-written data.
```

**Choosing the lock service:**

| Service | Guarantees | Use for |
|---|---|---|
| Redis `SET NX PX` + token | Mutual exclusion *while the lease holds and nobody pauses*; cheap; low latency | Rate limiting of a single action, idempotent rebuilds, coarse single-writer tasks where the resource is idempotent anyway |
| Postgres advisory locks (transaction-scoped) | True mutual exclusion, transactional, auto-release on crash, no lease to expire | Anything already inside a DB transaction. The best answer when you have a database |
| Postgres `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` / atomic `UPDATE ... WHERE` | True exclusion with row-level scope | Per-row/per-entity work: the default answer |
| etcd / ZooKeeper / Consul | Consensus, linearizable reads, real leader election with a lease and a revision number | Leader election, critical global coordination, anything where a split brain is unacceptable |
| Redis with Redlock | Contested; published analysis shows the failure mode above | Prefer single-instance Redis or a consensus store |

Note the honest engineering conclusion, which is also the intellectually satisfying one: **a distributed lock with a lease can only ever provide "at most one *live* holder", never "at most one holder over time". The gap is closed by fencing tokens at the resource, or by making the critical section idempotent so a double execution is harmless.** Most teams choose the second, because it is simpler: if running the report twice produces the same report, you do not need a lock at all, you need idempotency.

**Common mistakes**
1. `SETNX` followed by a separate `EXPIRE` (non-atomic).
2. No TTL on the lock.
3. `DEL` without a token check.
4. Not jittering the retry loop.
5. Treating a lease as a mutex. (Fencing or idempotency.)
6. Using a Redis lock for something a unique index already guarantees.
7. Holding a lock across a long network call without renewal, or without noticing the lease has already expired.
8. Using Redis Cluster without hash tags for a multi-key lock (e.g. lock + a metadata key) → `CROSSSLOT`. Use `{lock:report}:owner` and `{lock:report}:fence`.
9. Reusing the same lock key for two different logical locks, so unrelated work serializes.
10. A lock failure mode chosen implicitly. "If Redis is down, do we block or proceed?" must be an explicit, documented, alerted decision per lock.

**Interviewer probes**
- "Is a Redis lock safe?" → Safe for mutual exclusion while the lease holds and no process stalls; not safe against pauses, and Redlock's own documentation says so. The correct answer is fencing tokens or idempotency.
- "Your job ran twice and charged customers twice." → No idempotency key. Add one; then the lock becomes an optimization rather than the correctness mechanism.
- "How would you implement leader election?" → etcd/consul lease with a monotonically increasing revision; the leader holds a lease and renews it; on lease loss it stops; side effects are fenced by the revision, or the work is idempotent.
- "Why does `pg_advisory_xact_lock` beat a Redis lock when you already have a DB?" → Transactional scope, auto-release on crash, no lease to expire, no second system in the critical path, and it participates in the same atomicity as your data.

---

## 6.17.6 Redis data structures: strings, hashes, sets, sorted sets, streams

**Definition.** Redis's structures and, more importantly, the *shape* each one implies for memory, atomicity, and the operations you can do in O(1) versus O(log n) versus O(n).

| Structure | Commands | Complexity | Use for | Memory notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **String** | `GET SET SETNX MSET INCR DECR INCRBYFLOAT APPEND SETEX PSETEX GETSET` | O(1) | Cached serialized values, counters, rate-limit tokens, locks, idempotency keys | A serialized JSON blob has per-field overhead; a hash of 20 small fields is often cheaper than the JSON string of the same object |
| **Hash** | `HSET HGET HGETALL HDEL HMSET HINCRBY HSCAN` | O(1) per field | Object caches with partial updates; per-field counters | `HGETALL` on a huge hash blocks Redis; use `HSCAN` with `COUNT` |
| **List** | `LPUSH RPUSH LPOP RPOP LRANGE LTRIM BLPOP LMPOP BRPOPLPUSH` | O(1) ends, O(n) middle | Simple queues (with caveats), recent-activity lists, LRU lists via `LPUSH`+`LTRIM` | `LRANGE 0 -1` on a 1M list is a blocking operation that stalls the single thread |
| **Set** | `SADD SREM SMEMBERS SISMEMBER SINTER SUNION SDIFF SADD` O(1) add | Set ops O(N) | Tags, unique visitors, permissions, "seen" sets, dedupe | `SMEMBERS` on 1M members blocks; use `SSCAN` |
| **Sorted set (ZSET)** | `ZADD ZRANGEBYSCORE ZRANGEBYSCORE WITHSCORES ZREVRANGEBYSCORE ZRANGEBYRANK ZINCRBY ZREMRANGEBYSCORE ZRANGEBYLEX ZUNIONSTORE ZPOPMIN` | O(log N) add, O(log N + M) range | Leaderboards, rate-limit sliding windows, priority queues, delay queues | The single most useful structure for rate limiting; `ZRANGEBYSCORE ... LIMIT` is O(log N + M) |
| **Bitmap** | `SETBIT GETBIT BITCOUNT BITOP BITPOS` | O(1) | Feature flags per user, daily sign-in, presence over a bounded window | 1 bit per user per flag: 100M users × 1 flag = 12.5 MB |
| **HyperLogLog** | `PFADD PFCOUNT PFMERGE` | O(1) add, ~O(1) count | Approximate unique counts (UV) | 12 KB per HLL regardless of cardinality; standard error 0.81% |
| **GEO** | `GEOADD GEOSEARCH` | O(log N) | Nearby search | Redis 6.2+ uses a sorted set under the hood |
| **Stream** | `XADD XREAD XREADGROUP XACK XLEN XINFO XRANGE XTRIM XAUTOCLAIM` | O(1) append, O(log N) read | Durable queues, event streams, consumer groups | 6.17.9 |

**The rules that keep Redis healthy:**
- **`KEYS *` is banned in production.** It is O(N) and blocks the single thread for the duration. Use `SCAN` (cursor-based, incremental), `HSCAN`/`SSCAN`/`ZSCAN` for members, or maintain an explicit index key (a set of known keys) if you need to enumerate.
- **`SMEMBERS`/`HGETALL`/`LRANGE 0 -1` on a large collection blocks the server.** Same family of bug.
- **Big values cost you twice**: the keyspace is single-threaded, so a 10 MB value blocks every other client for the transfer time. Keep values small (target under 10 KB; hard ceiling around 1 MB), put bulk in object storage, and store an S3/GCS key in Redis.
- **`UNLINK` over `DEL`** for large values: `UNLINK` frees the memory in a background thread, `DEL` frees it synchronously.
- **Transactions (`MULTI/EXEC`) are not ACID.** No rollback, no isolation; commands are executed in order without interleaving from *other* clients. (An `EXEC` can return per-command errors, and other clients' commands *cannot* interleave inside, which is the actual guarantee.)
- **Lua scripts give you atomicity.** A script runs to completion with no other client interleaving, which is what makes compare-and-delete and check-and-increment correct. Keep them short: a slow script blocks the whole server, and in Redis 7 there is an `EVALSHA`/`FUNCTION` distinction plus script effects replication.
- **`SCAN` guarantees:** returns elements present throughout the full iteration, may return duplicates, may return nothing new. Design for that (idempotent processing).
- **Memory policy per instance**, from 6.17.3.
- **`maxmemory` accounting:** `INFO memory` → `used_memory`, `used_memory_rss`, `mem_fragmentation_ratio` (a high ratio means the allocator is fragmented; `activedefrag` helps), `evicted_keys` (must be zero on a `noeviction` instance and near-zero by design on a cache).

**Sorted sets deserve a worked example, because they solve rate limiting and leaderboards cleanly:**

```bash
# Leaderboard: O(log N) per update, O(log N + M) to read the top M
ZADD leaderboard:weekly 1500 "user:1" 1490 "user:2" 1620 "user:3"
ZREVRANGE leaderboard:weekly 0 9 WITHSCORES        # top 10
ZRANGEBYSCORE leaderboard:weekly 1500 +inf          # everyone above 1500
ZRANK leaderboard:weekly "user:2"                  # my rank (O(log N))
ZINCRBY leaderboard:weekly 5 "user:1"              # update my score
ZREMRANGEBYRANK leaderboard:weekly 0 -1001         # trim to top 1000
ZUNIONSTORE leaderboard:global 2 leaderboard:weekly leaderboard:alltime WEIGHTS 2 1

# Delay queue / scheduled work
ZADD schedule 1717200000 "job:42"                  # score = unix timestamp (when)
ZRANGEBYSCORE schedule -inf $(date +%s) LIMIT 0 100 # due now; claim with ZPOPMIN or ZREM
```

```ts
// Pipeline + multi for bulk work: one round trip, still not atomic across clients
const p = redis.pipeline();
for (const id of ids) p.zincrby('article:42:views', 1, String(id));
await p.exec();

// Transaction for a multi-command atomic unit
await redis.multi().set('a', '1').incr('counter').exec();
```

**When Redis is the wrong tool:** you need secondary-index queries by anything other than the key; you need joins; you need durability stronger than AOF `everysec` for a value you cannot recompute; you need multi-key transactions with rollback; the data exceeds memory and you have no eviction story.

---

## 6.17.7 Redis persistence: RDB, AOF, and what you lose in each

**Definition.** RDB is a **point-in-time binary snapshot** of the whole dataset, taken on a schedule or on a save trigger. AOF (Append-Only File) is a **sequential log of every write command**, replayed on startup. Each loses something different.

| Dimension | RDB | AOF `everysec` | AOF `always` | AOF `no` |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Format | Binary snapshot (compact, fast to load) | Text/RESP command log | Same | None |
| Capture point | Only at snapshot time | Up to 1 second of writes lost | Zero writes lost (fsync per write) | Everything lost |
| Write cost | Fork + COW; can double memory transiently; blocks writes on a big fork | One fsync per second | fsync per write: catastrophic latency on rotational or network storage | None |
| Load time | Fast (seconds to minutes) | Slower (replay) | Same | n/a |
| File growth | One file, rewritten | Appends forever unless rewritten | Same | n/a |
| Corruption blast radius | The snapshot file only | The tail of the log; `redis-check-aof --fix` truncates to the last valid command | Same | n/a |
| RPO | Up to `save` interval (minutes) | ~1 second | 0 | Total |
| Best for | Caches (recomputable), large datasets, fast restarts | Sessions, queues, anything with a small RPO | Nothing, honestly, in modern cloud | Pure cache |

```conf
# RDB
save 3600 1        # save if >= 1 key changed in the last hour
save 300 100      # save if >= 100 keys changed in the last 5 min
save 60 10000     # save if >= 10000 keys changed in the last minute
dbfilename dump.rdb
dir /var/lib/redis
rdbcompression yes
rdbchecksum yes
stop-writes-on-bgsave-error yes     # do NOT silently accept writes you cannot snapshot

# AOF
appendonly yes
appendfsync everysec        # the pragmatic setting
no-appendfsync-on-rewrite yes
auto-aof-rewrite-percentage 100
auto-aof-rewrite-min-size 64mb
aof-use-rdb-preamble yes    # store the base as RDB, append as AOF: faster load
```

**What you actually lose in each, stated precisely:**
- **RDB:** everything since the last snapshot. If you can recompute the data (a cache), that is free. If not, it is a real RPO measured in minutes.
- **AOF `everysec`:** up to 1 second of writes. On a crash of the Redis *process*, the data on disk is at most 1 second stale. If Redis runs on an instance that disappears (node failure, AZ outage), the last fsync may be older — the durability guarantee is only as good as your disk.
- **AOF `always`:** nothing, at the cost of a disk fsync per write (0.1 to 5 ms, and catastrophic on network storage). Use it when the RPO is zero *and* the storage is a local NVMe with a battery-backed cache. Then understand that `fsync` is still lying if the drive lies.
- **Neither saves you from a bad write.** AOF faithfully replays `FLUSHALL`, `SET key <wrong>`, and the `INCR` that overflowed. `redis-check-aof` will not tell you the value was wrong.
- **Neither replaces a backup.** Redis's persistence protects against a process crash; it does not protect against `flushall`, a corrupted file, an accidental `CONFIG SET dir`, or a region loss. Ship periodic RDB/backup copies to object storage.

**The fork problem and `stop-writes-on-bgsave-error`.** `BGSAVE` forks the process; the child writes the snapshot while the parent's pages are copy-on-write. On a large dataset with heavy writes, COW can double memory usage (Redis docs recommend memory headroom above ~50% of the dataset) and the fork itself briefly stops the event loop (longer with a huge dataset and a big `writev`/`uwsgi` page table). `stop-writes-on-bgsave-error yes` is a safety feature: if the last save failed (disk full), Redis refuses writes rather than running blind. That produces an availability outage, which is why you alert on `rdb_last_bgsave_status` and on disk space, rather than turning it off.

```bash
redis-check-rdb dump.rdb          # validate
redis-check-aof --fix appendonly.aof   # validate, and truncate to the last valid command
```

**A concrete durability decision to state in an interview:** "Our cache instance runs with `appendonly no` and `maxmemory-policy allkeys-lru`, because every key is recomputable from Postgres; losing the cache costs one round of stampedes and we have SWR plus single-flight to absorb it. Our session store runs `appendfsync everysec` in a Sentinel/HA topology with 2 replicas accepting writes, so a single node loss costs at most one second of new sessions; for a compliance-relevant session we would move to `always` on local NVMe. Our rate-limiter instance runs `appendonly no` deliberately, because rate-limit counters do not need to survive a restart, and an AOF fork on a high-cardinality keyspace would cost more than it buys."

---
