## 6.17.8 Redis Cluster, Sentinel, and replication

**Definition.**
- **Replication**: a primary and N replicas, each with a full copy. One writer (unless you deliberately allow replica writes, which is a footgun).
- **Sentinel**: a supervision process (or three, for quorum) that monitors, notifies, and performs automatic failover for a **single-instance** Redis. It does not shard and does not proxy; clients need a Sentinel-aware client library.
- **Cluster**: sharding across 6,384 hash slots with automatic failover, one primary plus replicas per slot range. The cluster bus handles gossip and failure detection.

### Replication

```conf
# On the replica
replicaof 10.0.1.10 6379
masterauth <password>
replica-read-only yes
repl-diskless-sync yes          # diskless replication: the replica asks for the data stream
repl-diskless-sync-delay 5
repl-backlog-size 64mb
repl-backlog-ttl 3600
min-replicas-to-write 1         # refuse writes if no replica is healthy
min-replicas-max-lag 10         # ... and only if it is within 10s
```

| Property | Value |
|---|---|
| Replication model | Asynchronous, single master → N replicas |
| Read from replicas | `replica-read-only yes` (default): yes, but **stale** by the replication lag |
| Failover | Manual (`replicaof no one`, then repoint clients) unless Sentinel is present |
| **Read-your-writes** | **Not guaranteed.** A read from a replica immediately after a write can miss it. Mitigations: write to master, read from master when the session wrote recently; or use a monotonic read strategy (see below); or `WAIT` |
| `WAIT n timeout` | Blocks until `n` replicas have acknowledged the write, or `timeout` ms elapse. Converts async replication into a tunable durability/visibility guarantee at the cost of latency. `WAIT 1 100` is a common "make this write visible on a replica" idiom |
| Partial resync | Uses the replication backlog; a replica that is only briefly disconnected gets the delta rather than a full sync |
| Full resync | `repl-diskless-sync yes` streams the RDB over a socket; otherwise the primary writes a temp RDB to disk and the replica reads it |
| Cascade | Not automatic; you can make a replica itself replicate from another replica |
| **No multi-key atomicity across replicas** | A `MULTI/EXEC` on the primary is atomic there; a replica applies it in order, but a *read* from a replica can see a partial... no, a replica applies the transaction's commands in the same order, so it cannot see a partial transaction *from that source*; however it can be arbitrarily behind, so it can see an older state of *both* keys |
| Monotonic reads | `client-replica-read-only` plus the `-READONLY` command and tracking (`CLIENT TRACKING`, `REDIRECT`) lets a client be served by the same replica for a session, which prevents seeing data go backwards |

### Sentinel

```conf
# sentinel.conf
sentinel monitor mymaster 10.0.1.10 6379 2      # 2 = quorum of 3 sentinels must agree
sentinel auth-pass mymaster <password>
sentinel down-after-milliseconds mymaster 5000   # declare down after 5s without a reply
sentinel failover-timeout mymaster 60000
sentinel parallel-syncs mymaster 1               # resync replicas one at a time after failover
```

| Property | Sentinel |
|---|---|
| Scope | One master + its replicas. **No sharding** |
| Who fails over | The sentinels, by quorum (2 of 3), so a single sentinel does not act alone |
| Client awareness | The client library asks the sentinels for the current master (`SENTINEL get-master-addr-by-name`) and reconnects. A naive client talking to a fixed IP will not follow a failover |
| Split brain | Mitigated by the quorum, but a network partition that leaves 1 sentinel vs 2 still requires the majority side to win; run sentinels on independent failure domains |
| Self-healing | Configurations are rewritten in the Sentinel config file and propagated to the other sentinels |
| DNS | Do not rely on DNS; use the sentinel-aware client |

### Cluster

```
Slots: 0..16383, 16384 total.
Key slot: CRC16(key) mod 16384.
Hash tags: {user:42}:profile and {user:42}:entitlements both hash by "user:42".
```

| Property | Cluster |
|---|---|
| Sharding | 16,384 slots mapped across masters; each master has replicas |
| Routing | Client asks for a node, gets `-MOVED`/`-ASK`, caches the slot map. A correct client follows redirects transparently |
| `MOVED` vs `ASK` | `MOVED` = permanent, update the slot map. `ASK` = the key is being migrated, serve it from the target node *for this request only* and never cache it |
| Multi-key ops | All keys in one slot. `CROSSSLOT` otherwise. Use hash tags for related keys |
| Atomicity | Single-slot `MULTI/EXEC` and Lua; cross-slot is not available |
| Failover | Automatic per master; replicas promoted by the cluster's majority-based failure detection |
| Rebalancing | `CLUSTER RESHARD` or `redis-cli --cluster rebalance`; online, but adds latency |
| Complexity | Real. Clients must be cluster-aware; `KEYS`/big `SCAN`s get slower; debugging requires `CLUSTER INFO`, `CLUSTER NODES`, `CLUSTER SLOTS` |
| **When you need it** | The dataset genuinely does not fit in one node's memory *with headroom*, i.e. hundreds of GB to multiple TB of live data, or you need horizontal write scaling across nodes |

```bash
redis-cli cluster info
redis-cli cluster nodes
redis-cli cluster slots
redis-cli -c -h node1 get user:42           # -c = follow redirects
redis-cli --cluster create node1:6379 node2:6379 node3:6379 --cluster-replicas 1
redis-cli --cluster check 10.0.1.10:6379
```

**Decision table:**

| Need | Choose |
|---|---|
| A cache, one instance, evictable | Single Redis, `allkeys-lru`, Sentinel for HA (or a managed equivalent), 1 primary + 1 replica |
| Sessions, moderate data | Sentinel topology, AOF `everysec`, replicas accepting failover |
| Rate limits / locks / counters | Separate instance, `noeviction`, Sentinel, small, no AOF if recomputable |
| Dataset > one node's memory, high QPS, horizontal scaling | Cluster (with hash tags and a cluster-aware client) |
| Availability without sharding, managed | ElastiCache / MemoryDB / Azure Managed Redis / GCP Memorystore, with cluster mode enabled and automatic failover |

**Cluster gotchas that appear in interviews:**
- **Slot concentration.** `user:{1}:*` for a giant customer occupies one slot's traffic on one node. Fix: shard the key (`user:{1}:0:*`, `user:{1}:1:*`) at the cost of losing single-slot atomicity.
- **`CROSSSLOT`** on `MGET a b` → use hash tags, or pipeline single-key gets (with the caveat that pipelining across slots is fine for reads but the multi-key atomicity is lost).
- **Resharding moves data** and slows both source and target during the migration.
- **A cluster-aware client is mandatory**, and a proxy (e.g. `redis-cluster-proxy`, or your own Envoy/HAProxy with a slot-aware routing layer) is often easier for non-Redis clients.
- **`UNLINK`/`FLUSHALL ASYNC` behaviour differs across cluster nodes**; a `FLUSHALL` on one master does not flush the cluster.

**Common mistakes**
1. Reading from replicas right after a write, breaking read-your-writes. (Use `WAIT`, a session-pinned replica, or read-your-writes routing.)
2. Running Sentinel without a Sentinel-aware client, so failover "does not happen" from the app's point of view.
3. `min-replicas-to-write`/`min-replicas-max-lag` unset, so a primary with zero healthy replicas keeps accepting writes it cannot replicate — silent single point of failure.
4. Cluster without hash tags, so every multi-key operation throws `CROSSSLOT`.
5. `maxmemory` equal to the instance RAM, so the kernel OOM-killer beats Redis's own eviction.
6. A big key (a 100 MB hash) on one node, causing an unbalanced slot and a latency spike on that node only.
7. Believing Sentinel shards. It does not; it supervises one master.
8. Using Cluster for a 4 GB dataset because it "scales better", and paying the complexity for nothing.
9. No `min-replicas-to-write` on a store whose loss would be unrecoverable (e.g. a queue used as the only copy of an event).

---

## 6.17.9 Redis Pub/Sub and Streams

**Definition.** **Pub/Sub** is fire-and-forget messaging: `PUBLISH` delivers to currently-connected `SUBSCRIBE` clients in O(N) and delivers to nobody who was not connected. **Streams** are an append-only log of entries with consumer groups, acknowledgements, and pending-entry tracking: a durable queue.

### Pub/Sub

```bash
SUBSCRIBE cache:invalidate            # receives messages
PSUBSCRIBE user:*                      # pattern subscribe
PUBLISH cache:invalidate "user:v1:42"  # message payload is a string; no history, no ack
```

| Property | Pub/Sub |
|---|---|
| Delivery | At-most-once, best effort, only to *currently connected* subscribers |
| Durability | **None.** A subscriber that was offline misses the message forever |
| Ordering | Per channel, in publish order, but interleaved across channels; no global order |
| Backpressure | None; the message is dropped for a slow subscriber |
| Cost | `O(connected subscribers)` per publish; the single thread does the sends |
| Throughput | Up to ~1M messages/s on a big instance, but latency degrades with payload size and subscriber count (buffer limits) |
| Use for | Fan-out notifications, cache invalidation hints, "something changed" signals, metrics/presence pings |
| Do **not** use for | Anything you must not lose: jobs, billing events, state transitions |

**The correct way to use Pub/Sub: as an optimization on top of a TTL.** Because delivery is not guaranteed, a Pub/Sub invalidation message is a *hint*. The TTL is the guarantee. This is the line to say in an interview: "Pub/Sub makes invalidation instantaneous when it arrives; the TTL is what makes the system correct when it does not."

```ts
// Publisher: after the DB commit
await redis.publish('cache:invalidate', `user:v1:${id}`);
// Subscriber: best-effort local eviction
subscriber.on('message', (channel, key) => localCache.delete(key));
```

**Redis 7's `CLIENT TRACKING` / invalidation push** is the more precise version of the same idea: the client tells Redis which keys it has read (`CLIENT CACHING YES`), and Redis pushes an invalidation message when one of those keys changes. That eliminates the polling and the key-encoding bugs in hand-rolled Pub/Sub invalidation. It still does not survive a disconnect; you still want a TTL.

### Streams

```bash
XADD events:orders '*' order_id 42 status paid     # auto id 1699999999999-0
XADD events:orders MAXLEN ~ 1000000 '*' order_id 43 status shipped   # approximate trim
XLEN events:orders

# Consumer group
XGROUP CREATE events:orders processors $           # create at the current tail
XGROUP CREATE events:orders processors 0           # or from the beginning (consume history)
XREADGROUP GROUP processors c1 COUNT 100 BLOCK 5000 STREAMS events:orders >

# Acknowledge (delete from the PEL)
XACK events:orders processors 1699999999999-0 1699999999999-1

# Recovery: what did I read but not ack?
XPENDING events:orders processors - + 10
XCLAIM events:orders processors c2 60000 1699999999999-0   # claim after 60s idle
XAUTOCLAIM events:orders processors c3 60000 0              # Redis 6.2+: batch auto-claim

# Inspect and trim
XRANGE events:orders - + COUNT 10
XTRIM events:orders MAXLEN ~ 1000000
XINFO GROUPS events:orders
```

| Property | Streams |
|---|---|
| Delivery | **At-least-once** (entries stay in the PEL until acked) |
| Durability | Bounded by `MAXLEN` trimming and by memory; with AOF, bounded by the AOF's fsync |
| Consumer groups | Load-balanced: each message goes to one consumer in the group |
| Recovery | `XAUTOCLAIM` reclaims entries from dead consumers after an idle timeout — this is what makes it a real queue and what you must implement a sweeper for |
| Ordering | Per stream, by entry ID; concurrent consumers process out of order, so **your handler must be idempotent and order-insensitive** |
| Trimming | `MAXLEN ~ N` (approximate, cheap, recommended) or `MINID` (drop by ID) |
| Multi-consumer | `XREAD` (fan-out to everyone) vs `XREADGROUP` (work queue) — pick deliberately |
| Backpressure | Consumers control their own rate via `COUNT`/`BLOCK`; unacked entries accumulate |
| Limits | Single stream can be large (millions of entries), but a hot key/group with no trimming is a memory leak; the PEL grows if consumers ack lazily |

```ts
// A correct Streams consumer: idempotent handler + reclaiming dead consumers
async function consume(group: string, consumer: string, handle: (e: StreamEntry) => Promise<void>) {
  const reclaim = setInterval(async () => {
    // Take over entries abandoned by a consumer that died, so the queue self-heals.
    const claimed = await redis.xautoclaim('events:orders', group, consumer, 60_000, '0', 'COUNT', 100);
    for (const [id, fields] of claimed[1]) {
      await handle({ id, fields });                      // handler must be idempotent
      await redis.xack('events:orders', group, id);
    }
  }, 30_000);

  for await (const res of redis.xreadgroup('GROUP', group, consumer, 'COUNT', 100, 'BLOCK', 5000, 'STREAMS', 'events:orders', '>')) {
    for (const [stream, entries] of res ?? []) {
      for (const [id, fields] of entries) {
        try {
          await handle({ id, fields });                   // idempotent!
          await redis.xack('events:orders', group, id);
        } catch (err) {
          logger.error({ err, id }, 'handler failed; leaving in PEL for reclaim');
          await deadLetter('events:orders', id, fields, String(err));
          await redis.xack('events:orders', group, id);  // do not retry forever
        }
      }
    }
  }
  clearInterval(reclaim);
}
```

**Streams vs Kafka/RabbitMQ/SQS vs Postgres:**

| Dimension | Redis Streams | Kafka | RabbitMQ | SQS | Postgres (LISTEN/NOTIFY, or a queue table) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Throughput | ~100k msg/s per instance | Millions/s | Tens of thousands/s | Tens of thousands/s | Thousands/s |
| Ordering | Per stream | Per partition | Per queue (single consumer) | Best-effort FIFO per message group | Per queue table |
| Retention | Memory + `MAXLEN` (bounded; use a consumer-group design) | Disk, days-to-years | Until acked | Up to 4 days | Until deleted |
| Replay | Yes (`XRANGE` from any ID, as long as not trimmed) | Yes, from any offset | No | No | Yes (until deleted) |
| Consumer groups | Yes, with reclaim | Consumer groups | Competing consumers | Visibility timeout | `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` |
| Exactly-once | No (at-least-once; make handlers idempotent) | Effectively-once with idempotent producers + transactions | At-least-once | At-least-once | Exactly-once *effects* if the handler and the state update share a transaction (`LISTEN/NOTIFY` caveat: notify is lossy on crash; a queue table with `SKIP LOCKED` is durable) |
| Ops burden | Low (you already run Redis) | High (or a managed service) | Medium | None (managed) | None (you already run the DB) |
| Best fit | Small-to-medium event volume, low latency, already have Redis | High-volume durable event log, replay, many consumers | Rich routing, work queues, low-to-medium volume | Fire-and-forget background work, zero ops | Small queues, transactional coupling with your data |

**The strongest interview answer on queues:** "Start with the transactional outbox in Postgres plus `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`. It gives me at-least-once delivery, idempotent consumers, no new infrastructure, and the state change and the enqueue are atomic. Move to Kafka when I need retention beyond memory, replay for many independent consumers, or throughput beyond a single node's. Use Redis Streams when the volume is small, the latency target is tight, and I already operate Redis and want one less system to page someone about."

**Common mistakes**
1. Using Pub/Sub for work that must not be lost. (The message goes to nobody if the consumer was down.)
2. `XACK` before the handler completes, losing the message on a crash.
3. No `XAUTOCLAIM`/`XCLAIM` sweeper, so a consumer crash leaves entries stuck in the PEL forever.
4. Unbounded stream growth (no `MAXLEN`), which is a memory leak that takes out the instance.
5. Assuming at-least-once means "the handler runs once". It runs *at least* once; without idempotency keys you double-charge someone.
6. `BLOCK 0` on `XREADGROUP` with no timeout, in a code path that must be cancellable, so shutdown hangs.
7. A single giant consumer group with 500 consumers on one stream, where the ordering guarantee you were relying on is gone because consumers process concurrently.
8. Redis Streams holding events that are legally required to be retained for 7 years.
9. Pub/Sub invalidation with no TTL, so a lost message is permanent staleness.
10. Publishing a large payload (a full JSON row) on every write, turning the single thread into a bottleneck for all clients.

---

## 6.17.10 Rate limiting algorithms: token bucket, leaky bucket, sliding window (CANONICAL)

**Definition.** A rate limiter enforces a bound on request rate. The four families differ in *what they bound* and therefore in what shape of traffic they permit.

| Algorithm | Bounds | Allows bursts | State per key | Memory | Redis implementation |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Fixed window** | Requests per aligned window (e.g. per minute, aligned to the clock) | **2× at boundaries**: 100 requests at 12:00:59 and 100 at 12:01:00 | 1 counter + window id | O(1) | `INCR` + `EXPIRE` |
| **Sliding window log** | Exact: at most N in any trailing W seconds | No burst beyond N | One timestamp per request in the window | **O(N)** — worst case | Sorted set |
| **Sliding window counter** | Approximate: weighted sum of two fixed windows | Smoothed, ≤ 2× at boundary in theory but weighted | 2 counters | O(1) | Hash or 2 keys |
| **Token bucket** | Average rate r with a burst capacity b | **Yes, up to b**, then sustained at r | tokens + last-refill time | O(1) | Hash + Lua |
| **Leaky bucket** | **Output** rate (smooths traffic to a fixed rate) | Queue-shaped: excess is queued or dropped | queue depth + last-leak time | O(1) or O(queue) | Hash + Lua |

### Fixed window

```lua
-- KEYS[1] = rate key, ARGV[1] = window seconds, ARGV[2] = limit
local window = redis.call('INCR', KEYS[1])
if window == 1 then redis.call('EXPIRE', KEYS[1], ARGV[1]) end
if window > tonumber(ARGV[2]) then return {0, 0} end
-- Retry-after = time until the current window ends
local ttl = redis.call('TTL', KEYS[1])
return {1, tonumber(ARGV[2]) - window, ttl}
```

**The flaws to state:** (a) a client can send `limit` requests at the end of window *n* and `limit` at the start of window *n+1*, i.e. 2× the limit in a very short period; (b) two requests in the same millisecond contend on one counter (fine) but a burst of 1,000 in one millisecond is entirely accepted; (c) the window boundary is a global cliff, so every client's limit resets at the same instant and traffic spikes every window.

### Sliding window log (exact)

```lua
-- KEYS[1] = zset, ARGV[1] = now(ms), ARGV[2] = window(ms), ARGV[3] = limit, ARGV[4] = member id
redis.call('ZREMRANGEBYSCORE', KEYS[1], '-inf', tonumber(ARGV[1]) - tonumber(ARGV[2]))
local count = redis.call('ZCARD', KEYS[1])
if count >= tonumber(ARGV[3]) then
  local oldest = redis.call('ZRANGE', KEYS[1], 0, 0, 'WITHSCORES')
  local retryAfter = tonumber(oldest[2]) + tonumber(ARGV[2]) - tonumber(ARGV[1])
  return {0, retryAfter}
end
redis.call('ZADD', KEYS[1], tonumber(ARGV[1]), ARGV[4])
redis.call('PEXPIRE', KEYS[1], tonumber(ARGV[2]))
return {1, tonumber(ARGV[3]) - count - 1, 0}
```

**Critical detail:** the zset member must be **unique**. Using a millisecond timestamp as the member means two requests in the same millisecond *overwrite* each other and the count under-counts, letting a client exceed the limit. Use `now:requestId` or a monotonic counter, or `redis.call('INCR', seqKey)` inside the same script. This is a real, exploited bug.

**Cost:** memory proportional to the number of requests in the window. 1,000 req/s with a 60s window = 60,000 members per key. At 10,000 keys that's 600M members. Use it only for low-volume, high-stakes limits (login attempts, password reset, API keys), and add a hard memory cap per instance with `noeviction` so an attacker cannot OOM you by generating keys.

### Sliding window counter (approximate, O(1) memory)

```lua
-- KEYS[1]=current window key, KEYS[2]=previous window key
-- ARGV[1]=now(ms), ARGV[2]=window(ms), ARGV[3]=limit, ARGV[4]=total requests so far
local w = math.floor(tonumber(ARGV[1]) / tonumber(ARGV[2]))
local cur  = redis.call('GET', 'rl:' .. ARGV[5] .. ':' .. w) or '0'
local prev = redis.call('GET', 'rl:' .. ARGV[5] .. ':' .. (w-1)) or '0'
local elapsed = (tonumber(ARGV[1]) % tonumber(ARGV[2])) / tonumber(ARGV[2])
local estimate = tonumber(cur) + tonumber(prev) * (1 - elapsed)
if estimate >= tonumber(ARGV[3]) then
  return {0, math.ceil((tonumber(ARGV[3]) - tonumber(cur)) / (tonumber(ARGV[3]) / tonumber(ARGV[2])))}
end
local n = redis.call('INCR', 'rl:' .. ARGV[5] .. ':' .. w)
redis.call('PEXPIRE', 'rl:' .. ARGV[5] .. ':' .. w, tonumber(ARGV[2]) * 2)
return {1, math.floor(tonumber(ARGV[3]) - estimate), n}
```

**Property:** the effective limit is bounded by `limit × (1 + w/W)` in the worst case but is smooth in practice; memory is two counters per key; the estimate is an interpolation between the previous and current window. This is the right default for high-volume, general-purpose API limiting.

### Token bucket (canonical implementation)

```lua
-- KEYS[1] = hash key for this limiter
-- ARGV[1] = now (ms, from the CLIENT so all nodes agree)
-- ARGV[2] = refill rate per second (float)
-- ARGV[3] = burst capacity (max tokens)
-- ARGV[4] = cost of this request (usually 1)
local data   = redis.call('HMGET', KEYS[1], 'tokens', 'ts')
local tokens = tonumber(data[1])
local ts     = tonumber(data[2])
if tokens == nil then tokens = tonumber(ARGV[3]); ts = tonumber(ARGV[1]) end

local elapsed = math.max(0, (tonumber(ARGV[1]) - ts) / 1000)   -- seconds; clamp negative
tokens = math.min(tonumber(ARGV[3]), tokens + elapsed * tonumber(ARGV[2]))

local allowed, retryAfter = 0, 0
if tokens >= tonumber(ARGV[4]) then
  allowed = 1
  tokens = tokens - tonumber(ARGV[4])
else
  retryAfter = math.ceil((tonumber(ARGV[4]) - tokens) / tonumber(ARGV[2]))
end

redis.call('HSET', KEYS[1], 'tokens', tostring(tokens), 'ts', tostring(tonumber(ARGV[1])))
-- TTL must cover the time to refill to full capacity, plus margin
redis.call('PEXPIRE', KEYS[1], math.ceil(tonumber(ARGV[3]) / tonumber(ARGV[2]) * 1000) + 60000)
return {allowed, retryAfter, tostring(math.floor(tokens))}
```

**Design points to explain:**
- **Two fields are required**: the current token count and the timestamp of the last update. Refill is computed from elapsed time, so an idle client does not accumulate unbounded tokens (capped at capacity) and does not need a timer.
- **The clock must come from one place.** If each node passes its own `Date.now()`, clock skew between nodes corrupts the refill math. Either pass the timestamp from the client (trusting clients can cheat *upward* to gain tokens) or use Redis's `TIME` command *inside* the script (correct and unimpeachable: `local t = redis.call('TIME')`).
- **`min(tokens, capacity)`** is the cap: an hour of idleness gives you exactly `capacity` tokens, not an hour's worth.
- **The TTL is a garbage-collection device**, set to slightly more than the time needed to refill to full. If the TTL is shorter, a partially-full bucket is deleted and the client gets a fresh full bucket (free quota).
- **Atomicity** is why this is a Lua script: read-modify-write of two fields must not interleave.

### Leaky bucket (output smoothing)

```lua
-- ARGV: now(ms), rate (per sec), capacity (queue depth), cost
local level = tonumber(redis.call('GET', KEYS[1]) or '0')
local level = math.max(0, level - ((now - last) / 1000) * rate)  -- leak over time
if level + cost <= capacity then
  redis.call('SET', KEYS[1], level + cost, 'PX', ttl)
  return {1, 0}                     -- accepted, will be released at `rate`
else
  return {0, ceil(cost / rate)}     -- rejected (or accepted-and-queued, your choice)
end
```

**The distinction to state crisply:** a token bucket controls the **rate at which requests enter** and lets bursts through (tokens accumulate); a leaky bucket controls the **rate at which requests leave** and smooths a burst into a steady drip (queued). Use a token bucket for user-facing API limits (be generous to legitimate bursty clients); use a leaky bucket in front of a fragile downstream that cannot handle bursts (a legacy payment gateway, a partner with a hard concurrency limit, an email sender).

### The cross-cutting decisions

**Per-key or global?** Per-API-key, per-user, per-IP, per-tenant, per-endpoint — or global. Multi-dimensional limits are usually "min of several buckets": `allowed = min(user_bucket, ip_bucket, global_bucket)`. The IP bucket is what stops a distributed enumeration attack; the user bucket is what stops one abusive account; the global bucket is your cost protection.

**Which key?** For anonymous traffic, the client IP (careful: behind a proxy, use `X-Forwarded-For` only from trusted proxies, and prefer a hashed IP to avoid PII in Redis). For authenticated traffic, the user or the API key. For cost protection, the tenant.

**Fail open or fail closed?**
- **Fail closed** for authentication, password reset, MFA, and anything where being permissive under attack is worse than being unavailable: login, signup, OTP, payment initiation.
- **Fail open** for reads and for non-abuse-relevant endpoints, with the observation that a Redis outage then means unbounded traffic to the database — so you must have load shedding ready (6.10.3).
- Whatever you choose: a config flag, a metric, and an alert on the flag. A fail-open flag stuck on during an incident is how a Redis blip becomes a bill.

**Distributed or local?** A local (in-process) limiter is exact for a single instance and free, but a fleet of N instances multiplies the global limit by N. Options: (a) divide the limit by N and use a local limiter (fast, approximate globally, correct per instance); (b) a Redis limiter (accurate globally, ~0.3ms per check); (c) a hybrid: local limiter for the fast path plus a periodic global reconciliation. The interview answer: **local for coarse protection, Redis for the authoritative per-identity limit.**

**Multi-region?** Either a home-region limiter (extra cross-region latency, a hole while the home region is down) or per-region limits divided by region count (no extra latency, and the global limit is exactly what you configured). State which one and what the resulting global limit is.

**Response headers and status code.** Return `429 Too Many Requests` with `Retry-After` (seconds, integer) and, ideally, the three community-standard headers:
```
RateLimit-Limit: 100          # the quota
RateLimit-Remaining: 42        # what is left in this window
RateLimit-Reset: 37            # seconds until reset
Retry-After: 37                # for 429s
```
Also make it observable: a metric per limiter of `allowed`, `limited`, and `limited_by_dimension`, so "why am I being rate limited" is answerable and a misconfigured limiter is visible before it becomes a support ticket.

**Common mistakes**
1. Sliding-window-log zset member = timestamp, so same-millisecond requests overwrite and the limit is exceeded.
2. Client-supplied timestamps without clamping, so a client with a fast clock gets free tokens (and one with a slow clock locks itself out).
3. Token bucket TTL shorter than the refill time, so a partially-drained bucket expires and the client gets a full one.
4. Missing `min(tokens, capacity)`, so an idle client accumulates a huge burst.
5. `INCR` without `EXPIRE` (fixed window), leaving keys forever — a slow memory leak.
6. Rate limiting after authentication, so the login endpoint's *authentication* work is unmetered.
7. Limiting by IP behind a corporate NAT or a carrier NAT, locking out thousands of legitimate users.
8. One global bucket in front of every endpoint, so a chatty analytics client starves everyone.
9. Failing closed on reads during a Redis outage, turning a cache-adjacent outage into a total outage.
10. Not alerting on the limiter's rejection rate, so a bug in the key derivation (a `null` becoming `"undefined"`) puts every user in one shared bucket.

**Interviewer probes**
- "Which algorithm for a public API?" → Token bucket, per API key, with a documented burst; sliding-window counter if you want to also constrain the sustained average without bursts; the answer includes the numbers (`rate=10/s, burst=30`) and the headers.
- "Why is the fixed window exploitable?" → Two full limits can pass within milliseconds across the boundary; state the fix.
- "How do you limit login attempts?" → Sliding window log, keyed on a hash of IP plus a hash of the email, a low limit (5 per 15 min), fail **closed**, with an exponential backoff and a lockout notification rather than a hard block (to prevent credential-stuffing lockout attacks against real users).
- "Redis is down. Now what?" → Per-endpoint decision: authentication endpoints fail closed with a clear 503; read endpoints fail open with load shedding and a bypass flag; the flag is alerted. And the local limiter keeps per-instance protection so a Redis outage cannot become an unbounded-traffic outage.

---

## 6.17.11 Session storage and the session-store decision

**Definition.** A session is server-side state keyed by an opaque identifier in the client. The decision is *where* that state lives: in the application's memory, in a database, in Redis, in a signed cookie, or in a client-side store.

| Option | Where state lives | Server restart | Horizontal scale | Revocation | Size limit | Use when |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **In-process memory** | The app server | All sessions lost | Requires sticky sessions | Easy (kill the entry) | Heap | Single-process tools, tests, CLIs |
| **Database table** | Postgres/MySQL | Survives | Any instance can read | Immediate (`DELETE`) | Row size, fine | Low-to-medium session counts, or sessions with a strong audit requirement |
| **Redis** | Redis | Survives (RDB/AOF) | Any instance | Immediate (`DEL`) | Practical ~a few KB per session | The default for most production web apps |
| **Signed/encrypted cookie** | The **client** | N/A | Perfect | **Only by expiry** — cannot revoke a bearer token | ~4 KB (cookie size limit) | Stateless APIs, JWT-like designs where revocation is handled separately |
| **Hybrid: signed cookie id + Redis** | Client has the id, server has the state | Survives | Any instance | Immediate | Small cookie | The pragmatic default |
| **JWT / self-contained token** | The client, signed | N/A | Perfect | Needs a denylist, a short TTL, and refresh-token rotation | ~4 KB | Stateless APIs and third-party/mobile clients where cookies are impractical |

```sql
-- Postgres sessions: explicit, auditable, and fine up to a few hundred thousand concurrent
CREATE TABLE sessions (
  id           text PRIMARY KEY,           -- 256 bits of entropy, base64url
  user_id      bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  data         jsonb NOT NULL DEFAULT '{}',
  created_at   timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
  last_seen_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
  expires_at   timestamptz NOT NULL,
  ip_hash      text,
  user_agent   text
);
CREATE INDEX ON sessions (user_id);
CREATE INDEX ON sessions (expires_at);    -- for the cleanup sweep
-- Cleanup: a scheduled DELETE, or PG 14+ on a partitioned table
DELETE FROM sessions WHERE expires_at < now();
```

```ts
// Redis sessions: TTL is the expiry, so no cleanup job
async function createSession(userId: string, data: object): Promise<string> {
  const sid = crypto.randomBytes(32).toString('base64url');
  await redis.set(`sess:${sid}`, JSON.stringify({ userId, ...data }), {
    EX: SESSION_TTL_SECONDS,                       // absolute expiry
  });
  return sid;
}
async function touchSession(sid: string) {
  // Sliding expiry, only if we want it; refresh at most every N minutes to avoid
  // turning every request into a write.
  await redis.expire(`sess:${sid}`, SESSION_TTL_SECONDS, 'NX');
}
```

**The properties that decide it, in order:**
1. **Revocation requirement.** Can you log a user out *right now*, everywhere, including from a stolen token? If yes, you need server-side state (or a denylist), so cookies-with-server-state or short-lived JWTs plus a refresh-token denylist.
2. **Horizontal scaling.** In-process sessions require sticky sessions, which couples your load balancer to your app deployment and make a node loss a session-loss event for every user on it.
3. **Size and shape.** Sessions holding a shopping cart or a wizard's partial state can exceed the 4 KB cookie limit fast. Redis or a DB is required.
4. **Cost.** Redis: ~1 KB per session in memory; 100k active sessions ≈ 100 MB. Postgres: a row plus indexes, and every session read is a query you pay for on the hot path (mitigated by pooling; still more expensive than Redis and it adds to your primary's load).
5. **Compliance and audit.** If sessions must be auditable or revokable centrally, a database table wins; Redis is not an audit log.
6. **Failure behaviour.** Redis down ⇒ session validation fails. Decide: fail closed (503, "please retry") for authenticated pages, or a stateless fallback (accept a signed-but-unverified cookie? never) — the answer is a short-lived "grace token" or accepting the outage. Note that this is one of the strongest arguments for putting *only an identifier* in the cookie and the state in Redis, so the failure is a clean 503 rather than a security hole.

**The security rules:**
- Session id: **128 bits of CSPRNG entropy**, never sequential, never derived from anything guessable. 32 bytes from `crypto.randomBytes`.
- Cookie flags: `HttpOnly` (no JS access ⇒ no XSS theft), `Secure` (TLS only), `SameSite=Lax` or `Strict` (CSRF mitigation), `Path=/`, a narrow `Domain` (or none), and `__Host-` prefix (which requires `Secure`, no `Domain`, and `Path=/`) to prevent subdomain cookie-tossing.
- **Rotate the session id on privilege change** (login, password change, MFA completion) to defeat session fixation.
- **Idle timeout and absolute timeout**, both enforced server-side. An absolute timeout of, say, 12 hours means a stolen token cannot be used indefinitely regardless of activity.
- **Bind to something** when it matters: IP hash and user-agent for high-risk actions (not for every request, because mobile networks change IPs constantly and you would log people out).
- **Do not put anything secret in the session** that the user could edit — with server-side sessions, store what you need, not what is convenient.
- **Never put PII or roles you trust without revalidating** in a client-side JWT without an expiry; and always verify the signature with an explicit algorithm and an allowed-algs list (the `alg: none` and RS256→HS256 confusion attacks).

**When a stateless JWT is right, and the price:**
- **Right when:** many independent clients (mobile, third-party, CLI), no session store at all in the architecture, and the revocation requirement is soft (a 15-minute access token plus a refresh token you *can* revoke server-side).
- **The price:** a JWT cannot be revoked before it expires, so you either accept that window (keep access tokens short: 5 to 15 minutes), maintain a denylist (which reintroduces state), or use short-lived tokens plus refresh-token rotation with reuse detection (which is the current best practice and which you should be able to describe: refresh token stored hashed in a database; on use, rotate it and revoke the entire family if a previously-used refresh token is presented).

**Common mistakes**
1. Sequential or low-entropy session ids.
2. Not setting `HttpOnly`, so one XSS exfiltrates every session.
3. Sliding expiry that never actually happens because `EXPIRE` on every request (write amplification) or, worse, is never called, so sessions expire mid-use.
4. Sticky sessions as the only scaling plan, with no session store, so a deploy logs out every user.
5. Sessions in Postgres with no `expires_at` index, so the cleanup job is a sequential scan on a hot table.
6. A JWT with a 30-day expiry and no revocation story.
7. Storing the session id in `localStorage` (XSS-readable) instead of a cookie.
8. Trusting `X-Forwarded-For` from any source when deriving the session's IP binding.
9. Assuming a database session table scales; it does, but it puts every authenticated request on the primary's connection pool, and that pool competes with real work.
10. Never rotating the session id on login (session fixation).

---

## 6.17.12 HTTP and CDN caching headers

**Definition.** HTTP caching is a protocol between client, proxy, and origin, expressed in headers. **Private** caches (browsers) may store a response for one user; **shared** caches (CDNs, reverse proxies) store one copy for everyone, which is why a `Set-Cookie` or a `Vary` mistake on a shared cache is a data leak.

### The header set you must know cold

| Header | Meaning | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `Cache-Control` | The directives | The modern header; prefer it over `Expires` and `Pragma` |
| `Age` | How long the response has been in a shared cache | Set by the cache, not the origin |
| `ETag` | An opaque validator for the current representation | Strong (`"abc"`) vs weak (`W/"abc"`); pair with `If-None-Match` |
| `Last-Modified` | Timestamp validator | Weaker than an ETag; second-resolution |
| `Vary` | Which request headers affect the representation | `Vary: Accept-Encoding, Cookie` — a misconfiguration multiplies objects |
| `Age` + `Warning` | Diagnostics | `110 Response is Stale` is the classic signal |
| `Surrogate-Control` / `CDN-Cache-Control` | Cache directives aimed specifically at the CDN | Lets the CDN cache something the browser must not |
| `Cache-Control: no-store` | Do not store at all | The only directive that forbids storage (private caches too) |
| `Cache-Control: no-cache` | Store it, but **revalidate before use** | Confusingly named; it is not "do not cache" |
| `Cache-Control: must-revalidate` / `proxy-revalidate` | Once stale, do not serve stale | Also required by `stale-while-revalidate`/`stale-if-error` to have any force |
| `Stale-While-Revalidate: 60` | Serve stale for up to 60s while refreshing in the background | The CDN version of SWR |
| `Stale-If-Error: 300` | Serve stale for up to 300s if the origin errors | Excellent protection against origin outages |
| `s-maxage: 300` | Shared caches only; overrides `max-age` | |
| `immutable` | The resource will not change during its freshness lifetime | Correct for content-hashed assets; wrong for HTML |
| `Age` | Seconds since the response was generated | |

### The freshness model, stated properly

```
freshness_lifetime = s-maxage (shared) or max-age (private)
current_age        = Age + (now - response_time)
fresh?             = current_age < freshness_lifetime
```
Then **revalidation**: if stale (or `no-cache`), the cache sends a conditional request:
```
If-None-Match: "v7-abc"
```
and the origin replies `304 Not Modified` (no body) if the validator matches, or `200` with a full body otherwise. Revalidation is cheap (no body transfer) but not free (a request to the origin). This is why `ETag` + a short `max-age` is a common pattern for HTML: 30s fresh, then a 304.

### A worked set of headers for a few real cases

```http
# 1. A content-hashed JS bundle: cache forever, locally and at the edge
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Content-Type: application/javascript; charset=utf-8
Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable
ETag: "a1b2c3"
# The filename itself must contain the hash: app.4f3a2b1c.js

# 2. A CDN-cacheable product page that differs per currency
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Cache-Control: public, s-maxage=60, stale-while-revalidate=300, stale-if-error=86400
CDN-Cache-Control: public, s-maxage=60
Vary: Accept-Encoding
ETag: "page-1234-v3"

# 3. An authenticated page: private, never in a shared cache
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Cache-Control: private, no-store
Set-Cookie: session=...; HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Lax; Path=/; __Host-session
Vary: Cookie

# 4. A user-specific API response: private, briefly revalidated
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Cache-Control: private, max-age=30, must-revalidate
ETag: W/"orders-7-1712345678"
# Client sends: If-None-Match: W/"orders-7-1712345678"  ->  304

# 5. An API response that must never be cached anywhere
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Cache-Control: no-store
Pragma: no-cache          # HTTP/1.0 legacy, harmless
Expires: 0                # HTTP/1.0 legacy, harmless

# 6. A redirect
HTTP/1.1 301 Moved Permanently
Location: https://example.com/new
Cache-Control: public, max-age=3600
```

**The four mistakes that leak data, in order of how often they happen:**
1. **A personalized response served with `Cache-Control: public`** to a shared cache. One user's name served to another. Fix: `private` (or `no-store`) on anything that varies by user, and be conservative — default to `private` and opt *into* `public` deliberately.
2. **A missing `Vary` on a representation that depends on a request header** (`Accept-Encoding`, `Accept-Language`, `Cookie`, `Authorization`, a custom `X-Tenant`). Without `Vary`, the CDN serves the gzip variant to a client that cannot read it, or one tenant's page to another.
3. **Caching a response to a `POST`/non-idempotent request** because the CDN was configured to cache by URL only. This is how a "cached" payment POST goes out four times.
4. **`Vary: *`** — legal but means "never cache", and it is a signal you should have used `no-store` deliberately rather than accidentally.

**Query strings and cache keys.** The CDN's cache key includes the query string by default, which means `?utm_source=...` fragments your cache and destroys the hit ratio. Configure the CDN to **ignore** known tracking parameters and to **sort/normalize** remaining parameters. Conversely, if any parameter is security-relevant (`?token=`, `?signature=`), the key must include it *and* the response must be `private`.

**Cache-busting strategy for assets:**

| Strategy | How | Trade-off |
|---|---|---|
| Content hash in the filename | `app.4f3a2b1c.js` + `immutable` | Best: no revalidation, tiny HTML, perfect caching. Requires a build step that renames |
| Query string | `app.js?v=4f3a2b1c` | Simple, but the HTML changes every deploy and some CDNs cache the query string sloppily |
| Folder/path | `/static/v4/app.js` | Simple, folder-level invalidation |

**Layered TTLs, which is the real answer for a content site:**

| Layer | Directive | Typical |
|---|---|---|
| Browser | `max-age` | Static assets: 1 year, immutable. HTML: 0 or 30 |
| CDN | `s-maxage`, `stale-while-revalidate`, `stale-if-error` | Public content: 60 to 300s, SWR 300s, SIE 1 day |
| Application cache (Redis) | `EX` with jitter | Matches or exceeds the CDN's freshness, or slightly less, so the CDN is the outer layer that gets hits |
| Origin (Postgres) | n/a | Always correct |

**The rule of thumb that makes it coherent:** the CDN's TTL should be **less than or equal to** your application cache's TTL, and both should be justified by the staleness contract. If your Redis cache is 10 minutes and the CDN is 10 seconds, the CDN adds nothing but a hop; if the CDN is 10 minutes and Redis is 30 seconds, you have the same data cached twice at different freshness levels, which is fine but redundant.

**Surrogate keys / purge (advanced).** Fastly, Cloudflare, and Akamai support **surrogate keys**: tag cached objects, then purge by tag. This is the CDN equivalent of event-driven invalidation: `Surrogate-Key: product-42 category-7`, then `PURGE` by tag on write. It is the correct way to invalidate a CDN-cached HTML page without purging everything. Correctness still needs `max-age` as the backstop, because a purge can be lost.

**Conditional requests from your own application** (a different but adjacent skill): `If-None-Match`/`If-Modified-Since` to avoid re-downloading an unchanged bundle, `Range`/`Accept-Ranges` for resumable downloads, and `If-Match`/`If-None-Match` for **optimistic concurrency on writes** — `If-Match: "v3"` means "only apply this write if the resource is still at v3", which is an HTTP-native `If-Unmodified-Since`/`ETag` optimistic lock. That is a genuinely useful thing to know and it comes up in API design interviews.

**Common mistakes**
1. `public` on a personalized response. Data leak.
2. No `Vary: Accept-Encoding` (or `Vary: Cookie`) ⇒ broken responses or leaks.
3. `no-cache` used to mean "do not cache" ⇒ a revalidation request per hit.
4. Caching a non-idempotent response.
5. Tracking query parameters fragmenting the CDN cache key.
6. Not using content hashes, so every deploy invalidates a year of caching.
7. `Cache-Control: max-age=0` everywhere and calling it a CDN strategy; that is `no-cache` in effect.
8. Long CDN TTL with no `stale-if-error`, so an origin blip becomes a visible outage instead of a slow one.
9. Forgetting that `immutable` promises the bytes will never change; if the URL is not content-addressed, you have created a permanent stale-asset bug.
10. Purging the whole CDN on every deploy, which is a self-inflicted stampede against your origin.

---

## 6.17.13 Multi-layer caching coherence: the invalidation problem

**Definition.** Coherence is the property that all copies of a piece of data, across all cache layers, agree up to a stated bound. With one cache, "invalidation" is a `DEL`. With four layers (browser, CDN, reverse proxy, Redis, plus read replicas in the database), invalidation is a **distributed, at-least-once, best-effort** operation, and the system is correct only because every layer has a bounded TTL.

**The layers, outermost to innermost, and what invalidates each:**

| Layer | Typical lifetime | Invalidation mechanism | Can it be guaranteed? |
|---|---|---|---|
| Browser (private cache) | `max-age` | The server can only influence the *next* request | **No.** A user who never requests it keeps the stale copy; `Clear-Site-Data` clears it |
| CDN / edge | `s-maxage`, SWR, SIE | Surrogate-key purge, or TTL | **No.** Purges are best-effort and eventually consistent across PoPs |
| Reverse proxy / API gateway | seconds | Purge endpoint, or TTL | Usually no |
| Application L1 (in-process, e.g. Caffeine) | milliseconds to seconds | Local eviction + Pub/Sub/CLIENT TRACKING hint + TTL | **No**, without `CLIENT TRACKING` with broadcast invalidation |
| Redis (L2) | seconds to hours | `DEL`, versioned keys, outbox relay | **At-least-once** with an outbox; lossy with Pub/Sub |
| Read replicas | Replication lag | Nothing; you wait | Bounded by lag, which you monitor |
| Origin (Postgres) | — | — | **The truth** |

**The mental model that makes this tractable: TTLs must be monotonic from the outside in.**

```
browser max-age  ≤  CDN s-maxage  ≤  L2 Redis TTL  ≤  L1 local TTL(usually smallest)
```
Wait — that is backwards from intuition. The correct relationship: **inner layers may be fresher or staler, but the *outer* layer's TTL must be no longer than the staleness you have promised the user, and the inner layer must not be staler than the outer one, otherwise the outer cache is serving a fresher copy that the inner one will never see.** In practice: make each layer's TTL **shorter** as you go outward (browser/CDN shortest, because they are the hardest to invalidate and the most likely to be shared), and let the deep layers hold longer entries, refreshed by an explicit invalidation that is *more* reliable because it is closer to the writer. The practical statement that lands in an interview: **the harder a layer is to invalidate, the shorter its TTL, and every layer has a TTL as the backstop for the invalidation that will eventually be lost.**

### The mechanisms, ranked by reliability

**1. Versioned / namespaced keys (strongest, no coordination).**

```ts
// A write creates a new generation; readers switch to it. Old generations expire.
async function bumpGeneration(namespace: string) {
  const gen = await redis.incr(`gen:${namespace}`);      // atomic, monotonic
  return gen;
}
// Read: const gen = await redis.get(`gen:users`) ?? '1';
//       key = `users:${gen}:42`
// The old generation's entries are now unreachable and expire on their own TTL.
// Cost: 2x keys during the overlap window; memory spikes at the moment of a bump.
```
**Why it is the strongest:** there is no window in which a reader can see a stale value, because a reader either uses the old generation (consistent with the old data) or the new one. There is no "the delete was lost" failure mode. The failure mode is **memory** (two generations live) and **cache-miss storms** (the entire namespace is cold after a bump), which you handle with a background warmer. Bumping per *entity* rather than per namespace keeps the blast radius small: `gen:user:42`.

**2. Transactional outbox + relay (reliable, asynchronous).**

```sql
-- Same transaction as the business write
BEGIN;
UPDATE products SET price_cents = 995, updated_at = now() WHERE id = 42;
INSERT INTO outbox (topic, payload) VALUES
  ('cache.invalidate', '{"kind":"product","id":42,"keys":["product:v1:42","listing:v1:cat:7"]}');
COMMIT;
```

```ts
// The relay: at-least-once delivery, ordered per aggregate, idempotent consumers
for (;;) {
  const rows = await db.query(`
    SELECT id, topic, payload FROM outbox
    WHERE published_at IS NULL ORDER BY id LIMIT 200
    FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, []);
  if (rows.length === 0) { await sleep(200); continue; }
  for (const r of rows) {
    const keys: string[] = JSON.parse(r.payload).keys;
    // 1) Redis
    await redis.del(...keys);
    // 2) Invalidate the CDN by surrogate key (best effort; TTL is the guarantee)
    await purgeSurrogateKey(JSON.parse(r.payload).surrogateKey).catch(logAndContinue);
    // 3) Publish a hint for every app instance's L1 (best effort)
    await redis.publish('cache:invalidate', JSON.stringify(keys)).catch(logAndContinue);
    await db.query('UPDATE outbox SET published_at = now() WHERE id = $1', [r.id]);
  }
}
```

**Properties:** the invalidation cannot be lost by a process crash (it is in the same transaction as the write); it can be delivered late (relay lag — monitor it, alert on the oldest unpublished `outbox` row); it can be delivered twice (at-least-once, so consumers must be idempotent — `DEL` is idempotent, so this is free here). It does not cover writes that bypass the application (a `psql` fix, a bulk job that does not write to the outbox) — so you still need the TTL, and you should have a policy ("all writes go through the application or through a migration that emits outbox rows").

**3. Redis Pub/Sub / `CLIENT TRACKING` (best effort, instant when it works).** An optimization that makes invalidation *feel* immediate. Never the guarantee. Use `stale-while-revalidate` semantics so a missed message costs a little latency, not a wrong answer.

**4. TTL as the backstop (always).** Every layer, always, with jitter. Without this, one lost message is permanent staleness.

**5. Read-replica lag as an implicit layer.** If a read hits a replica and the replica is 2 seconds behind, then even a perfectly-invalidated Redis cache would be filled with 2-second-old data. So "cache coherence" includes "we do not read from replicas for the paths whose staleness budget is under the replication lag".

### The failure catalogue

| Failure | Symptom | Root cause | Fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| Lost invalidation | One user sees old data for hours | Process crashed between commit and `DEL`; or a code path that writes without invalidating | Outbox; a lint rule/test that asserts every write path invalidates; TTL |
| Invalidation before commit (race) | A reader repopulates the cache with the old value *after* the delete | `DEL` happened, then a concurrent reader read pre-commit state and wrote it back | Order: write DB → commit → `DEL`. Or versioned keys. Or write-through inside a transaction (still racy, but on the write path) |
| Partial invalidation of a derived key | The product page is updated, the category listing still shows the old price | One write → several cache entries, and you only invalidated one | **Compute the dependency list** when you build the key, and store it: a `cache_deps(cache_key, dep_key)` table, or an explicit "invalidate this family" convention. Best solution: make the derived value a **materialized view** in the DB, not a cache entry |
| CDN purge lost | Users globally see the old page past `max-age` | Purge API failed, or the PoP had a stale copy | `stale-if-error` + `must-revalidate`; monitor purge latency; surrogate keys + periodic full purge |
| `Vary` explosion | CDN hit ratio collapses | `Vary: Cookie` on a public page, so every user is an object | `private` + cookie-less CDN caching for the public shell; cache the *shell* publicly and the personal fragment privately |
| Cache-of-a-cache staleness | The CDN serves a page built from an L1 that was not invalidated when the CDN was purged | Purging the CDN re-triggers a render that reads a stale L1 | Purge the CDN *and* broadcast the invalidation; order matters (invalidate inner layers first, then purge the outer) |
| Thundering herd after a global version bump | Whole namespace cold, DB spikes | Version bump invalidates everything | Warm the top-N before flipping the generation pointer: populate generation *n+1* first, then bump the pointer. **Double-buffered generation swap** |
| Stampede from a shared key | See 6.17.4 | No SWR/single-flight | SWR + single-flight |
| Eventual consistency with no bound | "Sometimes it shows the old price" | Replication lag unbounded, or the CDN TTL is longer than the product's staleness budget | Instrument the actual staleness: measure `now - updated_at` in the response and alert on p99 |

### The design that actually holds together

```ts
// The full read path with a stated staleness contract, layered.
async function getProductPage(id: string, viewer?: Viewer) {
  // L1: in-process, microseconds, 2s TTL + broadcast invalidation
  const l1 = l1cache.get(`page:v1:${id}`);
  if (l1) return personalize(l1, viewer);

  // CDN: handled upstream; this response is what gets cached there.
  // The shell is public and long-cached; the personalized fragment is private.
  const base = await cacheAside(`page:v1:${id}`, {
    // L2: Redis, 120s TTL with ±20% jitter, SWR up to 600s
    compute: () => loadProductPageFromDb(id),
    staleWhileRevalidate: 600,
    maxStale: 3600,
  });

  // Personalization is applied AFTER the cacheable layer so the CDN can cache
  // the public part. The fragment is short-cached privately.
  return personalize(base, viewer);
}

function personalize(page: ProductPage, viewer?: Viewer) {
  const price = viewer?.region
    ? convertCurrency(page.priceCents, viewer.region)     // not part of the cached shell
    : page.priceCents;
  return { ...page, priceCents: price, canBuy: viewer ? canBuy(viewer, page) : null };
}
```

**The design principles to state, in order, as the section's closing argument:**
1. **The database is the only source of truth. Every cache is disposable.** If a cache cannot be dropped and rebuilt, it is not a cache; it is a second database with none of the guarantees of one.
2. **Every layer has a TTL. The TTL is the guarantee; invalidation is an optimization.** Every "we invalidate on write" claim is really "we invalidate on write, *and* it is gone within TTL". Be honest about this in an interview; it is the difference between a candidate and an engineer.
3. **The harder a layer is to invalidate, the shorter its TTL.** Browser and CDN: seconds to minutes. Redis and L1: minutes to hours with event-driven invalidation on top.
4. **Invalidate outward-in, invalidate inner-first.** Invalidate Redis, then purge the CDN, because purging the CDN causes an origin read that will repopulate Redis from whatever is there.
5. **Prefer versioned keys when the invalidation must be airtight** (authorization, money, entitlements), because it removes the "lost message" failure mode entirely.
6. **Make the derived value a database view, not a cache entry,** when it is expensive to compute *and* has many dependents. One materialized view has one invalidation story (the refresh); fifty cache keys have fifty.
7. **Measure staleness, do not assert it.** Put `X-Data-Age` (or an internal metric) on responses, alert on p99 staleness per endpoint, and make the staleness budget a number in a dashboard. A cache with no staleness metric is a cache whose failure you will discover from a user.

**Common mistakes (multi-layer specific)**
1. Believing `DEL` + Pub/Sub is a guarantee. It is at-least-once at best, and Pub/Sub is at-most-once.
2. No TTL "because we invalidate on write" ⇒ one lost message is permanent.
3. Purging the CDN but not Redis, so the re-render reads stale data and the purge is undone.
4. Invalidating Redis but not the CDN, so the purge of the outer layer repopulates it from a stale inner layer.
5. A derived cache key (`listing:v1:cat:7`) with no dependency tracking.
6. Personalization inside the cached layer, so either it is `private` and the CDN does nothing, or it is `public` and users see each other's data.
7. Cache key collisions across tenants (`product:42` with no tenant prefix) — the single most dangerous caching bug in multi-tenant systems, and it is an authorization bug, not a performance one.
8. Long CDN TTL on HTML that includes a personalized fragment.
9. A version bump that invalidates the whole namespace without a warmer, creating a self-inflicted stampede.
10. Never measuring the actual staleness, so the "eventual" in "eventually consistent" has no number.

---

# 6.8-6.17 MUST-IMPLEMENT-FROM-SCRATCH (12 Problems)

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

Every problem below is written to be typed out and run. Nothing here needs a framework; where Redis is used the Lua is given inline because the atomicity *is* the design. Sizes are chosen to be read, not to be fast — the correctness properties are what you are being tested on.

---

## Problem 1 — In-memory LRU + TTL cache with single-flight

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Why it is on the list:** it is the substrate every other cache builds on. If you cannot write a correct LRU with TTL and request coalescing, you cannot reason about a distributed cache either, because every distributed cache is the same thing plus a network hop and a failure mode.

**Requirements:** (1) O(1) get/set with LRU eviction under a max entry count; (2) per-entry TTL with **jittered** expiry; (3) single-flight so concurrent misses for the same key issue one load; (4) an explicit `maxStale` window for stale-while-revalidate; (5) negative caching; (6) metrics.

```ts
type Entry<T> = {
  value: T;
  freshUntil: number;
  staleUntil: number;
  hits: number;
};

export class LruTtlCache<T> {
  // Map preserves insertion order; re-inserting on read makes the first key the LRU.
  private map = new Map<string, Entry<T>>();
  private inflight = new Map<string, Promise<T>>();
  private refreshing = new Set<string>();
  private clock: () => number;

  constructor(
    private readonly max: number,
    private readonly ttlMs: number,
    private readonly staleMs: number,
    private readonly jitter = 0.2,
    clock: () => number = Date.now,
  ) { this.clock = clock; }

  private freshFor(base = this.ttlMs): number {
    const delta = base * this.jitter * (Math.random() * 2 - 1);   // ±jitter
    return Math.max(1, Math.round(base + delta));
  }

  private touch(key: string, e: Entry<T>): void {
    this.map.delete(key);
    this.map.set(key, e);                 // move to the most-recent end
  }

  private evict(): void {
    while (this.map.size > this.max) {
      const oldest = this.map.keys().next().value as string;
      this.map.delete(oldest);
      metrics.cache.evictions.inc({ reason: 'lru' });
    }
  }

  stats() { return { size: this.map.size, max: this.max, inflight: this.inflight.size }; }

  get(key: string): T | undefined {
    const e = this.map.get(key);
    if (!e) return undefined;
    if (this.clock() > e.staleUntil) {   // beyond even the stale window: a real miss
      this.map.delete(key);
      return undefined;
    }
    e.hits++;
    this.touch(key, e);
    metrics.cache.hits.inc();
    return e.value;
  }

  set(key: string, value: T): void {
    const now = this.clock();
    const fresh = this.freshFor();
    this.map.delete(key);
    this.map.set(key, { value, freshUntil: now + fresh, staleUntil: now + fresh + this.staleMs, hits: 0 });
    this.evict();
  }

  /** SWR read: fresh -> return; stale -> serve and refresh in background; absent -> block (once). */
  async fetch(key: string, load: () => Promise<T>): Promise<T> {
    const now = this.clock();
    const e = this.map.get(key);

    if (e && now < e.freshUntil) { this.touch(key, e); return e.value; }

    if (e && now < e.staleUntil) {
      if (!this.refreshing.has(key)) {
        this.refreshing.add(key);
        void (async () => {
          try { this.set(key, await load()); }
          catch (err) {
            logger.error({ err, key }, 'SWR refresh failed');
            metrics.cache.refreshFailures.inc();
            // Do NOT drop the entry: serving stale beats serving 500.
            const cur = this.map.get(key);
            if (cur) { cur.staleUntil = this.clock() + this.staleMs; }   // one grace extension
          } finally { this.refreshing.delete(key); }
        })();
      }
      this.touch(key, e);
      metrics.cache.staleServed.inc();
      return e.value;
    }

    // Cold. Coalesce.
    const existing = this.inflight.get(key);
    if (existing) { metrics.cache.coalesced.inc(); return existing; }

    const p = (async () => {
      // Re-check: another flight may have completed while we queued.
      const again = this.get(key);
      if (again !== undefined) return again;
      try {
        const v = await load();
        this.set(key, v);
        return v;
      } finally { this.inflight.delete(key); }
    })();
    this.inflight.set(key, p);
    return p;
  }

  delete(key: string): void { this.map.delete(key); }
  clear(): void { this.map.clear(); }
}
```

**Correctness notes you must be able to say out loud:**
- `Map` insertion order *is* the LRU order; a read re-inserts. Do not use `Object.keys` + sort per get (O(n log n)).
- **Jitter lives on `set`, not on `get`**, so the expiry is decided once at population time and every reader of that entry agrees on when it goes stale. If you jitter on read, readers disagree and you get inconsistent freshness.
- The stale window is a *separate* number from the TTL, so you can tune "how stale is acceptable" independently of "how often do we pay for a refresh".
- `inflight` and `refreshing` are separate because the cold path must **block** one caller while the stale path must **never block** any caller. Conflating them produces either a stampede or a hung request.
- Re-check inside the flight, because the second-arriving caller may have been queued behind a flight that already stored the value.

**Test it with:** an injected clock (`clock: () => fake.now`) so you can assert the fresh/stale/expired transitions without sleeping; a loader that increments a counter so you can assert one call for N concurrent `fetch`es; a max=2 cache to assert eviction order; and a loader that throws to assert the stale grace extension.

**Interviewer probes:** "Why jitter on write?" · "Why re-check inside the single-flight?" · "What happens if the loader throws on a cold miss?" (the flight rejects, all waiters reject, nothing is cached — so the next call retries, which is the right behaviour for a hard miss) · "How do you bound memory here?" (entry count, and value size is still on you).

---

## Problem 2 — Cache-aside Redis cache with SWR, stampede lock, negative caching

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Why:** this is the cache you will actually put in production, and every part of it exists because of a specific failure in Problem 4 of the theory section.

```ts
const SET_SCRIPT = `
  -- KEYS[1]=value key KEYS[2]=fresh key
  -- ARGV[1]=payload ARGV[2]=fresh seconds ARGV[3]=stale seconds
  redis.call('SET', KEYS[1], ARGV[1], 'EX', tonumber(ARGV[2]))
  -- 'fresh' marker is what lets the reader decide stale vs fresh without parsing JSON
  redis.call('SET', KEYS[2], '1', 'EX', tonumber(ARGV[2]) + tonumber(ARGV[3]))
  return 1`;

const RELEASE_SCRIPT = `
  if redis.call('GET', KEYS[1]) == ARGV[1] then
    return redis.call('DEL', KEYS[1])
  end
  return 0`;

const MISS = '\u0000MISS';

export class RedisCacheAside {
  constructor(private readonly redis: Redis, private readonly jw = 1) {}

  private async readRaw(key: string): Promise<{ v: unknown; fresh: boolean } | null> {
    const [raw, freshMarker] = await this.redis.mget(key, `${key}:fresh`);
    if (raw === null) return null;
    if (raw === MISS) return { v: null, fresh: true };
    // value present: 'fresh' marker present => within the fresh window
    return { v: JSON.parse(raw), fresh: freshMarker !== null };
  }

  async get<T>(key: string, load: () => Promise<T>): Promise<T> {
    const raw = await this.readRaw(key);
    if (raw) {
      if (raw.v === null) throw new NotFound();                  // negative cache hit
      if (raw.fresh) { metrics.cacheHit.inc(); return raw.v as T; }
      // Stale: serve it, refresh in the background behind a lock.
      void this.refreshUnderLock(key, load);
      metrics.staleServed.inc();
      return raw.v as T;
    }
    // Cold or beyond stale: try to get the lock; if we cannot, fall back to
    // a single-flight inside this process so at least one caller waits.
    return this.refreshUnderLock(key, load, /*blocking*/ true);
  }

  private async refreshUnderLock(key: string, load: () => Promise<T>, blocking = false): Promise<T> {
    const lockKey = `lock:${key}`;
    const token = crypto.randomUUID();
    const acquired = await this.redis.set(lockKey, token, { NX: true, PX: LOCK_TTL_MS });
    if (!acquired) {
      if (!blocking) return undefined as unknown as T;            // background path: give up
      // Blocking path: poll briefly, then read whatever is there (possibly stale).
      for (let i = 0; i < 20; i++) {
        await sleep(25 + Math.random() * 50);                     // jittered
        const again = await this.readRaw(key);
        if (again) return again.v as T;
      }
      throw new CacheFillTimeout(key);
    }
    try {
      const v = await load();                                      // compute
      await this.redis.eval(SET_SCRIPT, {
        keys: [key, `${key}:fresh`],
        arguments: [
          JSON.stringify(v),
          String(Math.round(TTL_SECONDS * (1 + 0.2 * (Math.random() * 2 - 1)))),   // fresh ±20%
          String(STALE_SECONDS),
        ],
      });
      return v;
    } finally {
      await this.redis.eval(RELEASE_SCRIPT, { keys: [lockKey], arguments: [token] });
    }
  }

  async invalidate(key: string): Promise<void> {
    await this.redis.del(key, `${key}:fresh`);                    // both, or the marker survives
  }

  async cacheNotFound(key: string, seconds = 5): Promise<void> {
    await this.redis.set(key, MISS, { EX: seconds });             // short: resources appear
  }

  // Write path. Order matters: commit FIRST, then invalidate. Invalidate-first
  // opens a window where a concurrent reader repopulates pre-commit state.
  async afterWrite<T>(work: () => Promise<T>, keys: string[]): Promise<T> {
    const out = await work();
    for (const k of keys) await this.invalidate(k);
    return out;
  }
}
```

**The four things an interviewer will check:**
1. **`afterWrite` commits before invalidating.** Invalidate-first is a lost-update race, not a safe ordering.
2. **The `:fresh` marker is deleted alongside the value.** A stale marker with a deleted value is harmless, but a marker with a new value makes a *fresh* value look stale (extra work, not a bug); deleting both is correct and simple.
3. **`blocking=false` on the stale path** so no reader ever waits on a refresh.
4. **The lock TTL (`LOCK_TTL_MS`) exceeds the worst-case compute time**, and the background path gives up immediately rather than piling up behind the lock.

**Trade-off to state:** polling in the blocking path adds latency under stampede but guarantees progress; the alternative (waiting indefinitely on the lock) converts a slow compute into an outage.

---

## Problem 3 — Token bucket rate limiter (Redis Lua), with a local fallback

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

```lua
-- KEYS[1] = bucket hash key
-- ARGV[1] = rate per second (float)   ARGV[2] = burst capacity
-- ARGV[3] = cost of this request       ARGV[4] = ttl ms override (optional)
local t = redis.call('TIME')            -- server clock: no client clock skew
local nowMs = (tonumber(t[1]) * 1000) + math.floor(tonumber(t[2]) / 1000)

local rate     = tonumber(ARGV[1])
local capacity = tonumber(ARGV[2])
local cost     = tonumber(ARGV[3])

local state  = redis.call('HMGET', KEYS[1], 'tokens', 'ts')
local tokens = tonumber(state[1])
local ts     = tonumber(state[2])

if tokens == nil then
  tokens = capacity                  -- a new bucket starts FULL (standard)
  ts = nowMs
end

-- Guard against a non-monotonic clock reading; never let tokens go negative or refill backwards
local elapsedMs = nowMs - ts
if elapsedMs < 0 then elapsedMs = 0 end
tokens = math.min(capacity, tokens + (elapsedMs / 1000) * rate)

local allowed = 0
local retryAfterMs = 0
if tokens >= cost then
  allowed = 1
  tokens = tokens - cost
else
  local deficit = cost - tokens
  retryAfterMs = math.ceil((deficit / rate) * 1000)
end

redis.call('HSET', KEYS[1], 'tokens', string.format('%.4f', tokens), 'ts', nowMs)
-- TTL = time to refill to full, plus margin. Shorter => a partially drained bucket
-- is deleted and the client is handed a free full bucket.
local ttlMs = ARGV[4] and tonumber(ARGV[4]) or (math.ceil((capacity / rate) * 1000) + 60000)
redis.call('PEXPIRE', KEYS[1], ttlMs)

return { allowed, retryAfterMs, string.format('%.0f', math.floor(tokens)) }
```

```ts
export async function limit(
  identity: string, action: string, opts: { rate: number; burst: number; cost?: number; failOpen?: boolean },
) {
  const key = `rl:{${action}}:${sha256(identity)}`;   // hash tag pins the key to one slot
  try {
    const [allowed, retryMs, remaining] = await redis.eval(TOKEN_BUCKET, {
      keys: [key],
      arguments: [String(opts.rate), String(opts.burst), String(opts.cost ?? 1)],
    });
    if (allowed === 1) return { allowed: true, remaining: Number(remaining) };
    return {
      allowed: false,
      retryAfterSec: Math.ceil(Number(retryMs) / 1000),
      headers: {
        'RateLimit-Limit': String(opts.burst),
        'RateLimit-Remaining': remaining,
        'RateLimit-Reset': String(Math.ceil(Number(retryMs) / 1000)),
        'Retry-After': String(Math.ceil(Number(retryMs) / 1000)),
      },
    };
  } catch (err) {
    // Explicit, per-endpoint failure policy.
    metrics.rateLimiter.errors.inc({ action, failOpen: !!opts.failOpen });
    if (opts.failOpen) return { allowed: true, degraded: true };
    throw new TooManyRequests('limiter unavailable');
  }
}

// A local limiter so a Redis outage cannot become an unbounded-traffic outage.
const local = new TokenBucketLocal({ rate: opts.rate / INSTANCE_COUNT, burst: opts.burst });
```

**Design points:** the hash tag `{action}` makes the key hashable to a single slot (needed for Cluster); the identity is **hashed** so PII is not in Redis; `redis.call('TIME')` removes clock skew; the `HSET` stores a formatted string so Lua and the client agree on precision; the TTL derivation is the subtle one and you should be able to explain why getting it wrong hands out free quota.

**The local fallback** is a token bucket with `rate / instanceCount`: exact per instance, approximately right globally, and it keeps working when Redis is down. That is the answer to "what happens when Redis is down" that shows operational maturity.

---

## Problem 4 — Sliding window counter and leaky bucket (Redis Lua)

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Sliding window counter** — O(1) memory per key, smooths the fixed-window boundary:

```lua
-- KEYS[1] = 'rl:{action}:<id>:<windowIndex>'  (current window)
-- KEYS[2] = same for the previous window
-- ARGV[1] = now ms  ARGV[2] = window ms  ARGV[3] = limit  ARGV[4] = action
local now      = tonumber(ARGV[1])
local windowMs = tonumber(ARGV[2])
local limit    = tonumber(ARGV[3])

local idx    = math.floor(now / windowMs)
local offset = now % windowMs

local cur  = tonumber(redis.call('GET', KEYS[1]) or '0')
local prev = tonumber(redis.call('GET', KEYS[2]) or '0')

-- Weight the previous window by how far we are into the current one.
local weight = 1 - (offset / windowMs)
local estimate = cur + prev * weight

if estimate >= limit then
  -- Time until enough of the current window's usage ages out.
  local excess = estimate - limit
  local retryMs = math.ceil((excess / limit) * windowMs)
  return { 0, retryMs, tostring(math.floor(limit - estimate)) }
end

local n = redis.call('INCR', KEYS[1])
redis.call('PEXPIRE', KEYS[1], windowMs * 2)      -- keep one extra window for `prev`
return { 1, 0, tostring(math.floor(limit - estimate - 1)) }
```

**Leaky bucket** — controls the *output* rate, so a burst becomes a steady drip for a fragile downstream:

```lua
-- KEYS[1] = queue-level key
-- ARGV[1] = now ms  ARGV[2] = leak rate per second  ARGV[3] = queue capacity  ARGV[4] = cost
local t = redis.call('TIME')
local nowMs = (tonumber(t[1]) * 1000) + math.floor(tonumber(t[2]) / 1000)

local last  = tonumber(redis.call('GET', KEYS[1] .. ':ts') or nowMs)
local level = tonumber(redis.call('GET', KEYS[1]) or '0')

local leaked = ((nowMs - last) / 1000) * tonumber(ARGV[2])
level = math.max(0, level - leaked)

local cost     = tonumber(ARGV[4])
local capacity = tonumber(ARGV[3])

if level + cost <= capacity then
  redis.call('SET',    KEYS[1],      string.format('%.2f', level + cost))
  redis.call('SET',    KEYS[1] .. ':ts', nowMs)
  redis.call('PEXPIRE', KEYS[1],      60000)
  redis.call('PEXPIRE', KEYS[1] .. ':ts', 60000)
  return { 1, 0, tostring(math.floor(capacity - level - cost)) }   -- accepted, released at rate
end
return { 0, math.ceil((level + cost - capacity) / tonumber(ARGV[2]) * 1000), '0' }
```

**The line to say:** "token bucket limits the rate *in* and lets bursts through; leaky bucket limits the rate *out* and smooths them. I use the token bucket for user-facing API limits and the leaky bucket in front of a partner service whose contract says 'no more than 5 requests per second, ever'."

**Note on the two-window sliding counter's accuracy:** the weighted estimate is an interpolation, so it can slightly under- or over-count around boundaries. State it as *approximate*; if you need exactness, use the zset sliding log and pay O(limit) memory per key.

---

## Problem 5 — Distributed lock with fencing tokens

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

```lua
-- Acquire: mint a fencing token and take the lock, atomically.
-- KEYS[1] = lock key, KEYS[2] = fencing counter (same hash slot via tags)
-- ARGV[1] = owner token, ARGV[2] = lease ms
local token = redis.call('INCR', KEYS[2])                 -- monotonic fencing token
if redis.call('SET', KEYS[1], ARGV[1] .. ':' .. token, 'NX', 'PX', ARGV[2]) then
  return { 1, tostring(token) }
end
return { 0, '0' }
```

```lua
-- Release: only the owner, compare-and-delete, one round trip.
local v = redis.call('GET', KEYS[1])
if v and string.sub(v, 1, string.len(ARGV[1]) + 1) == ARGV[1] .. ':' then
  redis.call('DEL', KEYS[1])
  return 1
end
return 0
```

```lua
-- Renew: extend the lease only if we still hold it. Required when the critical
-- section can outlast the initial lease.
local v = redis.call('GET', KEYS[1])
if v == ARGV[1] .. ':' .. ARGV[2] then
  return redis.call('PEXPIRE', KEYS[1], tonumber(ARGV[3]))
end
return 0
```

```ts
export class FencedLock {
  /** @param protectedFn receives the fencing token; pass it to every write to the resource. */
  async withLock<T>(name: string, leaseMs: number, fn: (fence: number) => Promise<T>): Promise<T> {
    const tag = `{lock:${name}}`;                    // one slot for lock + counter
    const lockKey = `${tag}:lock`;
    const fenceKey = `${tag}:fence`;
    const owner = crypto.randomUUID();
    const deadline = Date.now() + leaseMs;

    let fence: number;
    while (true) {
      const [ok, tok] = await redis.eval(ACQUIRE, { keys: [lockKey, fenceKey], arguments: [owner, String(leaseMs)] });
      if (ok === 1) { fence = Number(tok); break; }
      if (Date.now() > deadline) throw new LockTimeout(name);
      await sleep(30 + Math.random() * 70);           // jittered backoff
    }

    // Renew in the background if the work may outlast the lease.
    const renew = setInterval(async () => {
      const ok = await redis.eval(RENEW, { keys: [lockKey], arguments: [owner, String(fence), String(leaseMs)] });
      if (ok !== 1) lostLease = true;
    }, Math.floor(leaseMs / 3));

    try {
      return await fn(fence);
    } finally {
      clearInterval(renew);
      await redis.eval(RELEASE, { keys: [lockKey], arguments: [owner, String(fence)] });
    }
  }
}
```

**The resource side, which is the actual correctness mechanism:**

```sql
CREATE TABLE nightly_report (
  id    uuid PRIMARY KEY,
  fence bigint NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,      -- highest token ever accepted
  body  text,
  status text NOT NULL DEFAULT 'pending'
);

-- Every write from a lock holder carries its token; a stale holder is REJECTED.
UPDATE nightly_report
SET body = $2, fence = $3, status = 'complete'
WHERE id = $1 AND fence <= $3;
-- rows = 0 means "you are a zombie; your lease expired and someone else took over"
```

```ts
await locks.withLock('nightly-report', 60_000, async (fence) => {
  const res = await db.query(
    `UPDATE nightly_report SET body = $2, fence = $3 WHERE id = $1 AND fence <= $3`,
    [runId, renderedReport, fence],
  );
  if (res.rowCount === 0) {
    metrics.lockFencedRejections.inc();   // the metric that proves the mechanism works
    return;
  }
});
```

**The honest framing, which is the difference between a mid-level and a senior answer:** "A Redis lock gives me *at most one live holder*. It cannot give me *at most one holder over time*, because a lease can expire while the holder is stalled. The fencing token closes that gap at the resource. And if the critical section is idempotent — which mine is, because re-rendering a report produces the same report — then I do not need the lock at all; I need idempotency and the lock becomes an optimization. So the lock is only worth it when the effect is not naturally idempotent, and then the fence is mandatory."

---

## Problem 6 — Connection pool with leak detection, circuit breaker, and load shedding

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

```ts
import { Pool, type PoolClient } from 'pg';

export function createPool(url: string, size = 10) {
  const pool = new Pool({
    connectionString: url,
    max: size,
    idleTimeoutMillis: 30_000,
    connectionTimeoutMillis: 3_000,        // fail fast instead of hanging
    keepAlive: true,
    // Per-connection safety nets, applied to every session we hand out.
    statement_timeout: 15_000,
    idle_in_transaction_session_timeout: 60_000,
    application_name: 'orders-api',
  });
  pool.on('error', (err) => {
    // An *idle* client errored (server closed it). Log; never crash the process.
    logger.error({ err }, 'idle pool client error');
  });
  return pool;
}

const pool = createPool(process.env.DATABASE_URL!, 10);

/** Wrap every borrow so a leak cannot outlive the request. */
```
