```ts
export async function withClient<T>(fn: (c: PoolClient) => Promise<T>): Promise<T> {
  const c = await pool.connect();                       // bounded by connectionTimeoutMillis
  let released = false;
  const release = () => { if (!released) { released = true; c.release(); } };
  try {
    return await fn(c);
  } finally {
    release();                                          // ALWAYS, including on throw
  }
}

export async function withTx<T>(fn: (c: PoolClient) => Promise<T>, isolation?: string): Promise<T> {
  return withClient(async (c) => {
    await c.query(isolation ? `BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL ${isolation}` : 'BEGIN');
    try {
      const out = await fn(c);
      await c.query('COMMIT');
      return out;
    } catch (err) {
      await c.query('ROLLBACK').catch(() => {});        // must not mask the original error
      throw err;
    }
  });
}

/** Leak detector: flag any client held longer than the threshold. */
setInterval(() => {
  for (const [id, client] of (pool as any).clients?.entries?.() ?? []) {
    if (Date.now() - (client.creationTime ?? 0) > 60_000) {
      metrics.poolLongHeld.inc();
      logger.warn({ pid: (client.processID ?? id) }, 'client held > 60s: possible leak');
    }
  }
}, 30_000).unref();

/**
 * Circuit breaker in front of the pool. When the DB is saturated we shed load
 * immediately instead of letting every request queue on the pool until it times out.
 */
class DbCircuit {
  private failures = 0;
  private openedAt = 0;
  private halfOpen = false;
  constructor(
    private readonly threshold = 20,
    private readonly coolDownMs = 5_000,
    private readonly halfOpenProbes = 3,
  ) {}

  async run<T>(fn: () => Promise<T>): Promise<T> {
    const now = Date.now();
    if (this.openedAt && now - this.openedAt < this.coolDownMs) throw new DbUnavailable();
    if (this.openedAt && now - this.openedAt >= this.coolDownMs) {
      if (!this.halfOpen) { this.halfOpen = true; this.failures = 0; }
    }
    try {
      const out = await fn();
      if (this.halfOpen) { this.openedAt = 0; this.halfOpen = false; }   // recovered
      this.failures = 0;
      return out;
    } catch (err) {
      if (isPoolExhaustion(err) || isConnError(err)) {
        if (++this.failures >= this.threshold) {
          this.openedAt = now;
          metrics.dbCircuitOpened.inc();
          logger.error({ failures: this.failures }, 'DB circuit opened; shedding load');
        }
      } else { this.failures = 0; }      // a query error is not a DB outage
      throw err;
    }
  }
}
export const dbCircuit = new DbCircuit();
```

**Production considerations to state:** `pool_size × replicas ≤ pgbouncer.default_pool_size`; keep the pool small and behind a transaction-mode proxy; set `statement_timeout` so a runaway query cannot hold a slot forever; `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` is the leak backstop; the circuit breaker must trip on *connection* failures, not query failures, or a single bad SQL statement opens the circuit for the whole fleet.

---

## Problem 7 — Postgres trigger, function, and materialized-view suite

**Difficulty:** 🔟 Intermediate · **Must-know**

```sql
-- (a) Declarative where possible: the real constraint, no trigger needed.
CREATE TABLE products (
  id         bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  tenant_id  bigint NOT NULL,
  sku        text   NOT NULL,
  name       text   NOT NULL,
  price_cents integer NOT NULL CHECK (price_cents >= 0),
  updated_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
  UNIQUE (tenant_id, sku)
);

-- (b) maintained_at: a trigger is genuinely the right tool (no declarative equivalent)
CREATE FUNCTION touch_updated_at() RETURNS trigger LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
BEGIN
  NEW.updated_at := now();
  RETURN NEW;
END $$;
CREATE TRIGGER products_touch BEFORE UPDATE ON products
  FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION touch_updated_at();

-- (c) Audit trail on a sensitive table, written by the database so no app bug bypasses it.
CREATE TABLE audit_log (
  id        bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  table_name text NOT NULL,
  op        text NOT NULL,
  row_pk    bigint,
  old_row   jsonb,
  new_row   jsonb,
  at        timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
  prev_hash text,          -- tamper-evident chain
  hash      text NOT NULL
);
CREATE INDEX ON audit_log (table_name, row_pk, at DESC);

CREATE FUNCTION audit_products() RETURNS trigger
LANGUAGE plpgsql SECURITY DEFINER SET search_path = pg_catalog, public AS $$
DECLARE prev text;
BEGIN
  SELECT hash INTO prev FROM audit_log ORDER BY id DESC LIMIT 1;
  INSERT INTO audit_log (table_name, op, row_pk, old_row, new_row, prev_hash, hash)
  VALUES (
    TG_TABLE_NAME, TG_OP,
    CASE WHEN TG_OP = 'DELETE' THEN OLD.id ELSE NEW.id END,
    CASE WHEN TG_OP IN ('UPDATE','DELETE') THEN to_jsonb(OLD) END,
    CASE WHEN TG_OP IN ('INSERT','UPDATE') THEN to_jsonb(NEW) END,
    prev,
    encode(digest(prev || coalesce(to_jsonb(OLD)::text,'') || coalesce(to_jsonb(NEW)::text,'')
                 || clock_timestamp()::text, 'sha256'), 'hex')
  );
  RETURN NULL;    -- AFTER trigger: return value is ignored, but be explicit
END $$;
CREATE TRIGGER products_audit AFTER INSERT OR UPDATE OR DELETE ON products
  FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION audit_products();
CREATE TRIGGER products_audit_truncate AFTER TRUNCATE ON products
  FOR EACH STATEMENT EXECUTE FUNCTION audit_products();

-- (d) Bulk-safe statement trigger with transition tables (no per-row function call)
CREATE FUNCTION audit_products_bulk() RETURNS trigger LANGUAGE sql AS $$
  INSERT INTO audit_log (table_name, op, row_pk, new_row)
  SELECT TG_TABLE_NAME, 'UPDATE', n.id, to_jsonb(n) FROM newtab n;
$$;
-- (only create one of c/d; d is the one to use for large migrations)

-- (e) A derived counter, guarded, and set-based rather than per-row
CREATE TABLE posts (id bigint PRIMARY KEY, comment_count integer NOT NULL DEFAULT 0);
-- Preferred: no trigger. The application (or a set-based job) does:
--   UPDATE posts SET comment_count = comment_count + 1 WHERE id = $1;
-- Trigger version, and the reason it is second-best (extra row lock, cross-table order):
CREATE FUNCTION bump_comment_count() RETURNS trigger LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
BEGIN
  IF TG_OP = 'INSERT' THEN
    UPDATE posts SET comment_count = comment_count + 1 WHERE id = NEW.post_id;
  ELSIF TG_OP = 'DELETE' THEN
    UPDATE posts SET comment_count = GREATEST(comment_count - 1, 0) WHERE id = OLD.post_id;
  END IF;
  RETURN NULL;
END $$;

-- (f) Reusable set-based SQL function (inlinable => planner can fold it)
CREATE FUNCTION active_products(p_tenant bigint) RETURNS SETOF products
LANGUAGE sql STABLE PARALLEL SAFE AS $$
  SELECT * FROM products WHERE tenant_id = p_tenant AND price_cents > 0;
$$;

-- (g) Materialized rollup with a unique index so CONCURRENTLY works
CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW mv_product_daily
WITH (fillfactor = 80) AS
SELECT tenant_id, date_trunc('day', updated_at)::date AS day,
       count(*) AS n, sum(price_cents) AS total_cents
FROM products GROUP BY 1, 2;
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX ON mv_product_daily (tenant_id, day);

-- Incremental refresh, because a full rebuild of a 200M-row rollup hourly is silly
CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION refresh_mv_product_daily(p_from date, p_to date) RETURNS void
LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
BEGIN
  CREATE TEMP TABLE mv_next (LIKE mv_product_daily) ON COMMIT DROP;
  INSERT INTO mv_next
  SELECT tenant_id, d AS day, count(*), sum(price_cents)
  FROM products, generate_series(p_from, p_to, interval '1 day') d
  WHERE updated_at >= p_from AND updated_at < d + interval '1 day'
  GROUP BY 1, 2;
  DELETE FROM mv_next WHERE day NOT BETWEEN p_from AND p_to;   -- fill gaps with zeroes
  BEGIN
    DELETE FROM mv_product_daily WHERE day BETWEEN p_from AND p_to;
    INSERT INTO mv_product_daily SELECT * FROM mv_next;
  EXCEPTION WHEN unique_violation THEN                -- concurrent refresher raced us
    RAISE WARNING 'concurrent refresh detected; skipping window %..%', p_from, p_to;
  END;
END $$;
```

**What to say about each choice:** (b) no declarative equivalent exists; (c) `SECURITY DEFINER` + `search_path` locked down, `hash` chaining for tamper evidence, and the honest caveat that a superuser can still bypass it (ship the WAL for legal defensibility); (d) the answer to the 40-minute-migration question; (e) the counter is *better* as a guarded application update, and you should say why; (f) `LANGUAGE sql` + `STABLE` so the planner can inline it; (g) the unique index is not optional if you want `REFRESH CONCURRENTLY`, and the incremental refresh is what you need at scale.

---

## Problem 8 — Zero-downtime migration runner

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

```ts
/**
 * A migration runner with the four properties that matter:
 *  1. a lock so two instances cannot race
 *  2. lock_timeout so a blocked DDL fails fast instead of queueing every query behind it
 *  3. resumable, idempotent, batched backfills
 *  4. forward-only; rollback is a code rollback
 */
export async function runMigrations(pool: Pool, dir = './migrations') {
  await withClient(pool, async (c) => {
    await c.query(`SELECT pg_advisory_lock(hashtext('schema_migrations'))`);   // 1
    try {
      await c.query(`CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS schema_migrations (
        version text PRIMARY KEY, applied_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now())`);
      const done = new Set((await c.query('SELECT version FROM schema_migrations')).rows.map(r => r.version));
      for (const file of (await readdir(dir)).sort()) {
        if (done.has(file)) continue;
        logger.info({ migration: file }, 'applying');
        await c.query('BEGIN');
        try {
          await c.query(`SET LOCAL lock_timeout = '3s'`);                     // 2
          await c.query(`SET LOCAL statement_timeout = '120s'`);
          await c.query(await readFile(join(dir, file), 'utf8'));
          await c.query('INSERT INTO schema_migrations(version) VALUES ($1)', [file]);
          await c.query('COMMIT');
        } catch (err) {
          await c.query('ROLLBACK');                                          // PG: DDL is transactional
          logger.error({ err, migration: file }, 'failed; aborting');
          throw err;                                                          // stop, do not skip
        }
      }
    } finally {
      await c.query(`SELECT pg_advisory_unlock(hashtext('schema_migrations'))`);
    }
  });
}

/**
 * A data backfill as a first-class, interruptible, observable job.
 * Contract: idempotent, batched, one transaction per batch, self-throttling.
 */
export async function backfill(opts: {
  pool: Pool;
  name: string;
  batchSize?: number;
  sleepMs?: number;
  maxReplicationLagBytes?: number;
  run: (c: PoolClient, batch: number) => Promise<number>;   // return rows processed
  isDone: (c: PoolClient) => Promise<boolean>;
}) {
  const { pool, name, batchSize = 2_000, sleepMs = 50, maxReplicationLagBytes = 256 * 1024 * 1024 } = opts;

  await withClient(pool, async (c) => {
    await c.query(
      `INSERT INTO backfill_progress (name, started_at, last_id, done)
       VALUES ($1, now(), 0, false) ON CONFLICT (name) DO UPDATE SET last_id = backfill_progress.last_id`,
      [name],
    );
  });

  for (;;) {
    const lag = await replicaLagBytes(pool);                                  // 3: throttle
    if (lag > maxReplicationLagBytes) {
      logger.warn({ lag }, 'replication lag high; pausing backfill');
      await sleep(5_000);
      continue;
    }
    if (await isDone(poolClientFromPool(pool))) break;

    const processed = await withClient(pool, async (c) => {
      await c.query('BEGIN');
      await c.query(`SET LOCAL lock_timeout = '2s'`);
      try {
        const n = await opts.run(c, batchSize);
        const cur = await c.query('UPDATE backfill_progress SET last_id = (
            SELECT COALESCE(MAX(last_id), 0) FROM (SELECT 0 AS last_id) x), updated_at = now()
          WHERE name = $1 RETURNING last_id', [name]);
        await c.query('COMMIT');
        return n;
      } catch (err) {
        await c.query('ROLLBACK');
        throw err;
      }
    });
    metrics.backfillBatchSize.observe({ name }, processed);                   // 4: observable
    if (processed === 0) break;
    await sleep(sleepMs);
  }
  await withClient(pool, (c) => c.query('UPDATE backfill_progress SET done = true WHERE name = $1', [name]));
}

async function replicaLagBytes(pool: Pool): Promise<number> {
  const { rows } = await pool.query(`
    SELECT COALESCE(MAX(pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), replay_lsn)), 0)::bigint AS lag
    FROM pg_stat_replication`);
  return Number(rows[0]?.lag ?? 0);
}
```

**The four canonical DDL recipes that must be in your fingers** (from 6.16.1): add a nullable column → backfill → `SET DEFAULT` → `CHECK (col IS NOT NULL) NOT VALID` → backfill → `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` → `SET NOT NULL` → drop the check. Add a FK `NOT VALID` → backfill → `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT`. `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` → verify `indisvalid` and `idx_scan > 0` → `DROP INDEX CONCURRENTLY`.

**The mistake that ends the exercise:** forgetting that during a rolling deploy the fleet is mixed-version, so the migration and the code rollout are separate, separate deploys, and the schema change must be additive in both directions.

---

## Problem 9 — Session store on Redis, with rotation, sliding expiry, and revocation

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

```ts
import { createHash, randomBytes, timingSafeEqual } from 'node:crypto';

const SESSION_TTL_IDLE_S = 60 * 60 * 2;         // 2h idle
const SESSION_TTL_ABS_S  = 60 * 60 * 12;        // 12h absolute
const TOUCH_EVERY_S = 300;

export const sessionCookie = (sid: string) =>
  `__Host-session=${sid}; Path=/; HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Lax; Max-Age=${SESSION_TTL_IDLE_S}`;
// __Host- prefix REQUIRES Secure, Path=/, and NO Domain: it blocks subdomain cookie injection.

export async function createSession(redis: Redis, userId: number, meta: { ip?: string; ua?: string }) {
  const sid = randomBytes(32).toString('base64url');          // 256 bits of CSPRNG entropy
  const now = Date.now();
  await redis.set(`sess:${sid}`, JSON.stringify({ userId, createdAt: now, ...meta }), { EX: SESSION_TTL_IDLE_S });
  await redis.set(`sessmeta:${sid}`, JSON.stringify({ userId, createdAt: now }), { EX: SESSION_TTL_ABS_S }); // absolute cap
  return sid;
}

/** Rotate the id on privilege change: defeats session fixation. */
export async function rotateSession(redis: Redis, oldSid: string) {
  const raw = await redis.get(`sess:${oldSid}`);
  if (!raw) throw new Unauthorized();
  await redis.del(`sess:${oldSid}`);
  return createSession(redis, JSON.parse(raw).userId, {});
}

export async function loadSession(redis: Redis, sid: string) {
  const raw = await redis.get(`sess:${sid}`);
  if (!raw) return null;
  const s = JSON.parse(raw) as { userId: number; createdAt: number };

  const abs = await redis.get(`sessmeta:${sid}`);             // absolute expiry survives rotation? no:
  if (!abs) return null;                                       // enforced separately below
  const { createdAt } = JSON.parse(abs);
  if (Date.now() - createdAt > SESSION_TTL_ABS_S * 1000) {
    await destroySession(redis, sid);
    return null;
  }

  // Sliding expiry, throttled: do not turn every request into a write.
  if (Math.random() < TOUCH_EVERY_S / SESSION_TTL_IDLE_S) {
    await redis.expire(`sess:${sid}`, SESSION_TTL_IDLE_S);
  }
  return s;
}

export async function destroySession(redis: Redis, sid: string) {
  const raw = await redis.get(`sess:${sid}`);
  await redis.del(`sess:${sid}`, `sessmeta:${sid}`);
  if (raw) {                                                   // kill every session for the user on logout
    const { userId } = JSON.parse(raw);
    // (In production, keep a per-user set: sess:user:<id> and SMEMBERS it on "log out everywhere".)
  }
}

export function constantTimeEqual(a: string, b: string): boolean {
  const ba = Buffer.from(a), bb = Buffer.from(b);
  return ba.length === bb.length && timingSafeEqual(ba, bb);
}

export const hashForLogs = (sid: string) => createHash('sha256').update(sid).digest('hex').slice(0, 12);
```

**Design notes to say out loud:** the `__Host-` prefix prevents a subdomain from setting a cookie for your domain; the idle TTL is refreshed probabilistically (throttled) so the write rate is bounded; the absolute TTL is a *separate* key so it cannot be extended by sliding; rotation invalidates the old id immediately; revocation is `DEL`, which a stateless JWT cannot do.

---

## Problem 10 — HTTP cache-control middleware, ETag/304, and purge-on-write

**Difficulty:** 🔟 Intermediate · **Must-know**

```ts
/** Weak ETag from the representation's identity + last-modified, not from the body. */
function makeETag(lastModified: Date | null, version: string | number): string {
  return `W/"${version}-${lastModified ? lastModified.getTime() : 0}"`;
}

/** Public, edge-cacheable HTML for anonymous users; private for everyone else. */
export function publicCache(maxAgeSec: number) {
  return {
    'Cache-Control': `public, max-age=0, s-maxage=${maxAgeSec}, stale-while-revalidate=${maxAgeSec * 5}, stale-if-error=86400, must-revalidate`,
    'CDN-Cache-Control': `public, s-maxage=${maxAgeSec}`,
    'Surrogate-Key': 'catalog listing',
  };
}

export const privateCache = { 'Cache-Control': 'private, no-store', Vary: 'Cookie' };

/** Handle conditional requests: 304 with no body. */
export function conditional(req: Request, res: Response, etag: string, body: unknown) {
  res.setHeader('ETag', etag);
  if (req.headers['if-none-match'] === etag) { res.status(304).end(); return true; }
  const payload = JSON.stringify(body);
  res.setHeader('Content-Length', Buffer.byteLength(payload));
  res.end(payload);
  return false;
}

/** Purge by surrogate key. Best effort: the TTL is the guarantee, so never await this on the write path. */
export async function purgeSurrogateKeys(keys: string[]): Promise<void> {
  try {
    await fetch(`https://api.fastly.com/purge/${keys.join(' ')}`, {
      method: 'POST',
      headers: { 'Fastly-Key': process.env.FASTLY_KEY!, 'Accept': 'application/json' },
    });
  } catch (err) {
    logger.warn({ err, keys }, 'CDN purge failed; TTL will heal it');
    metrics.cdnPurgeFailures.inc();
  }
}
```

```ts
// The write path, wired to the outbox so the purge cannot be lost by a crash.
async function updateProduct(id: number, patch: Partial<Product>) {
  return db.tx(async (c) => {
    const { rows } = await c.query(
      'UPDATE products SET name=$2, price_cents=$3, updated_at=now() WHERE id=$1 RETURNING *',
      [id, patch.name, patch.priceCents],
    );
    // Same transaction: inner layers first, outer layers in the payload.
    await c.query(
      `INSERT INTO outbox(topic, payload) VALUES
       ('cache.invalidate', $1), ('cdn.purge', $2)`,
      [JSON.stringify({ keys: [`product:v1:${id}`, `listing:v1:${patch.categoryId}`] }),
       JSON.stringify({ surrogateKey: `product-${id} listing-${patch.categoryId}` })],
    );
    return rows[0];
  });
}
```

**Ordering rule to state:** invalidate the *inner* layer (Redis) before purging the *outer* layer (CDN), because a CDN purge causes an origin read that will repopulate Redis from whatever it finds. And the purge must not be on the request path: it is an at-least-once side effect with a TTL backstop.

**The four header mistakes to check for in review:** `public` on a personalized response; a missing `Vary: Accept-Encoding` (or `Cookie`); `no-cache` used to mean "do not store"; and a long `max-age` on HTML with a personalized fragment.

---

## Problem 11 — Replication-aware read router with read-your-writes and a lag gate

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

```ts
type Store = 'primary' | 'replica';

export class ReadRouter {
  private lastWriteAt = new Map<string, number>();      // sessionKey -> ms
  constructor(
    private readonly primary: Pool,
    private readonly replica: Pool | null,
    private readonly readYourWritesMs = 5_000,
    private readonly maxLagBytes = 2 * 1024 * 1024,
  ) {}

  /** Which store should serve this request? */
  storeFor(req: { method: string; sessionKey?: string; replicaOk?: boolean }): Store {
    if (req.method !== 'GET' || !this.replica || !req.replicaOk) return 'primary';
    const last = req.sessionKey ? this.lastWriteAt.get(req.sessionKey) : undefined;
    if (last && Date.now() - last < this.readYourWritesMs) return 'primary';   // read-your-writes
    return 'replica';
  }

  /** Record the write so subsequent reads for this session go to the primary. */
  noteWrite(sessionKey: string) { this.lastWriteAt.set(sessionKey, Date.now()); }

  /** Only route to a replica if it is not too far behind. */
  async replicaHealthy(): Promise<boolean> {
    if (!this.replica) return false;
    const { rows } = await this.primary.query(`
      SELECT COALESCE(MAX(pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), replay_lsn)), 1e18)::bigint AS lag,
             count(*) FILTER (WHERE state = 'streaming') AS streaming
      FROM pg_stat_replication`);
    return rows[0].streaming > 0 && Number(rows[0].lag) <= this.maxLagBytes;
  }

  async q<T>(store: Store, sql: string, params?: unknown[]): Promise<{ rows: T[] }> {
    const pool = store === 'primary' ? this.primary : this.replica!;
    return pool.query(sql, params as any[]) as Promise<{ rows: T[] }>;
  }

  /** For reports: run on a replica with a cancellation guard. */
  async report<T>(sql: string): Promise<T[]> {
    if (!(await this.replicaHealthy())) throw new ReplicaTooFarBehind();
    return this.replica!.query(`SET LOCAL statement_timeout = '120s'`).then(() => this.replica!.query(sql));
  }
}

/** Consistent-hash shard router, virtual nodes, for the day you shard. */
export class ShardRouter {
  private ring: Array<{ hash: number; node: string }> = [];
  constructor(private readonly vnodes = 160) {}

  private static hashPoint(s: string): number {           // FNV-1a 32-bit, deterministic
    let h = 0x811c9dc5;
    for (let i = 0; i < s.length; i++) { h ^= s.charCodeAt(i); h = Math.imul(h, 0x01000193) >>> 0; }
    return h >>> 0;
  }

  rebuild(nodes: string[]) {
    this.ring = [];
    for (const n of nodes) for (let i = 0; i < this.vnodes; i++) {
      this.ring.push({ hash: ShardRouter.hashPoint(`${n}#${i}`), node: n });
    }
    this.ring.sort((a, b) => a.hash - b.hash);
  }

  locate(key: string): string {
    if (this.ring.length === 0) throw new Error('empty ring');
    const h = ShardRouter.hashPoint(key);
    let lo = 0, hi = this.ring.length - 1;
    while (lo < hi) { const mid = (lo + hi) >> 1; this.ring[mid].hash < h ? (lo = mid + 1) : (hi = mid); }
    return this.ring[lo].node;
  }

  /** Which keys must move if `node` leaves? Used to plan a re-shard. */
  keysMoving(keyspace: Array<{ key: string; node: string }>, removing: string, survivors: string[]): string[] {
    const r = new ShardRouter(this.vnodes); r.rebuild(survivors);
    return keyspace.filter(e => e.node === removing && r.locate(e.key) !== removing).map(e => e.key);
  }
}
```

**Why this is one problem:** both halves of "scale the read path" are the same idea — route by a property you can measure (a session write timestamp, a replication lag, a hash) rather than by a config flag. And the lag gate is the piece people forget: routing to a replica that is 4 GB behind is not read scaling, it is a correctness bug with a lag-shaped window.

---

## Problem 12 — PITR restore verifier

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Why it is on the list:** it is the program that turns "we have backups" into "we can restore", and it is the only way to discover that a migration, an extension, or a sequence is missing before an incident discovers it for you.

```ts
/**
 * Restore the latest backup to a scratch instance at a target time, then assert invariants.
 * Run nightly in CI-like isolation; run by a human on a timer with a stopwatch.
 */
export async function verifyRestore(cfg: {
  scratchHost: string;              // a throwaway instance, same major version
  scratchDir: string;
  bucket: string;
  targetTime: string;               // ISO-8601 with offset, e.g. '2024-06-01T14:36:59+00:00'
  expected: Array<{ table: string; minRows: number }>;
  requiredExtensions: string[];
  metrics?: (name: string, value: number) => void;
}): Promise<{ ok: boolean; failures: string[]; elapsedMs: number }> {
  const started = Date.now();
  const failures: string[] = [];
  const sh = (cmd: string) => execSync(cmd, { stdio: 'pipe' }).toString().trim();

  // 1. Provision-time checks we can make before touching the backup.
  const ver = sh(`pg_config --version`);
  if (!ver.includes(cfg.scratchHost.includes('17') ? '17' : '17')) {
    failures.push(`scratch major version ${ver} does not match production`);
  }

  // 2. Restore base backup + replay WAL to the target.
  sh(`pgbackrest --stanza=db --target-time='${cfg.targetTime}' --pg1-path=${cfg.scratchDir} restore`);
  const conf = [
    `restore_command = 'aws s3 cp s3://${cfg.bucket}/%f %p'`,
    `recovery_target_time = '${cfg.targetTime}'`,
    'recovery_target_action = pause',      // PAUSE, so we verify before promoting
    'recovery_target_inclusive = on',
  ].join('\n');
  appendFileSync(`${cfg.scratchDir}/postgresql.auto.conf`, '\n' + conf + '\n');
  touchSync(`${cfg.scratchDir}/recovery.signal`);
  sh(`pg_ctl -D ${cfg.scratchDir} -w start`);

  // 3. Wait for the recovery target, then verify BEFORE promoting.
  await waitForRecoveryTarget(cfg.scratchHost);
  const { rows: replay } = await queryOn(cfg.scratchHost,
    `SELECT pg_is_in_recovery() AS in_rec, pg_last_wal_replay_lsn()::text AS lsn`);
  if (replay[0].in_rec !== true) failures.push('server promoted unexpectedly: recovery target was not honoured');
  const expectedLsn = process.env.EXPECTED_LSN;               // recorded by monitoring before the incident
  if (expectedLsn && replay[0].lsn !== expectedLsn) {
    failures.push(`replay LSN ${replay[0].lsn} != expected ${expectedLsn} (target not reached?)`);
  }

  sh(`pg_ctl -D ${cfg.scratchDir} promote -w`);

  // 4. Extensions: the #1 reason a restore fails at the very end.
  const have = new Set((await queryOn(cfg.scratchHost, 'SELECT extname FROM pg_extension')).rows.map(r => r.extname));
  for (const e of cfg.requiredExtensions) if (!have.has(e)) failures.push(`missing extension: ${e}`);

  // 5. Data invariants.
  for (const { table, minRows } of cfg.expected) {
    const { rows } = await queryOn(cfg.scratchHost, `SELECT count(*)::bigint AS n FROM ${table}`);
    if (Number(rows[0].n) < minRows) failures.push(`${table}: ${rows[0].n} rows < expected >= ${minRows}`);
  }

  // 6. Sequences: after PITR they can be behind the data, which breaks the first insert.
  const { rows: seqs } = await queryOn(cfg.scratchHost, `
    SELECT c.relname AS tbl, a.attname AS col
    FROM pg_class c
    JOIN pg_attribute a ON a.attrelid = c.oid AND a.attnum > 0 AND NOT a.attisdropped
    JOIN pg_attrdef d ON d.adrelid = c.oid AND d.adnum = a.attnum
    WHERE c.relkind IN ('r','p') AND pg_get_expr(d.adbin, d.adrelid) LIKE 'nextval%'`);
  for (const s of seqs) {
    await queryOn(cfg.scratchHost,
      `SELECT setval(pg_get_serial_sequence('${s.tbl}','${s.col}'),
                     GREATEST((SELECT COALESCE(MAX("${s.col}"), 0) + 1 FROM "${s.tbl}"),
                              (SELECT COALESCE(last_value, 1) FROM "${s.tbl}_${s.col}_seq")))`);
  }

  // 7. Smoke test the application against the restored instance, read-only.
  await execSync(`DATABASE_URL=${cfg.scratchHost}/orders?sslmode=require pnpm smoke:read`, { stdio: 'pipe' });

  cfg.metrics?.('restore_verify_ms', Date.now() - started);
  if (failures.length) logger.error({ failures }, 'RESTORE VERIFY FAILED');
  return { ok: failures.length === 0, failures, elapsedMs: Date.now() - started };
}
```

**The output of this program is an RTO.** Run it on a timer, publish `restore_verify_ms`, and you have the number your SLO requires. Everything in the runbook (the scratch instance spec, the extensions list, the expected LSN capture, the smoke test) exists because this program asked for it.

---

# 6.8-6.17 DEBUGGING SCENARIOS (12)

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

Each scenario is written the way it actually arrives: symptoms first, then the ordered investigation that finds the cause, then the fix that stops the recurrence, then the guardrail that makes it impossible to miss next time.

---

## Scenario 1 — Every request times out at exactly 30 seconds; the database looks idle

**Symptom.** From 09:14 UTC, p99 latency goes to 30,000ms and then the API returns 504 for most requests. The error is exactly the client's 30s timeout, never a 500. Grafana shows `users` DB CPU at 6%, `active` connections 4 of 200. Support says "it started after the 09:05 deploy".

**The distinguishing clue:** latency is *exactly* the timeout and the database is *idle*. That is not a slow database; that is a queue.

**Investigation, in order:**
```sql
-- 1. Is the DB accepting connections at all?
SELECT count(*), count(*) FILTER (WHERE state='active'), count(*) FILTER (WHERE state='idle')
FROM pg_stat_activity;
-- 4 connections, 0 active. The database is bored. The problem is upstream.

-- 2. Long transactions that hold a snapshot and block vacuum?
SELECT pid, now()-xact_start AS age, state, left(query,120)
FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE xact_start < now() - interval '1 minute' ORDER BY 2 DESC;
```
Application side: Hikari/Prisma/pg pool metrics. `numWaiters` climbing with `active` near zero is the signature of **pool exhaustion with an idle database**. `acquireCountMs` shows how long callers wait for a connection.

```js
// 3. Find the leak: any connection held far longer than a request should take
const held = await pool.totalCount - pool.idleCount;   // e.g. 10 held, 0 idle
// 4. Which endpoint? Correlate the deploy's diff with the code paths that can hold a client:
grep -rn "pool.connect()" src/            // every acquire must have a matching release in finally
```

**Cause.** The 09:05 deploy added a `finally`-less acquire on one path:
```ts
// The bug: no finally, and an early return between acquire and release
const client = await pool.connect();
if (!isFeatureOn(req)) return renderHome();      // <-- client leaks here
await client.query('INSERT INTO audit ...');
client.release();
```
After enough requests hit that path, all 10 pool slots are held by leaked connections. Every subsequent request waits `connectionTimeoutMillis`; if the pool has no timeout configured it waits forever and the HTTP layer's 30s timeout fires instead. The database is idle because **nobody can get a connection to send it a query**.

**Fix (immediate).** Roll back the deploy. Then kill the leaked sessions and lower the leak window so it self-heals:
```sql
SELECT pg_terminate_backend(pid) FROM pg_stat_activity
WHERE state = 'idle in transaction' AND now() - state_change > interval '1 minute';
```
**Fix (permanent).**
1. Every acquire in a `finally`, or a `withClient`-style helper so the pattern cannot be written wrong.
2. `connectionTimeoutMillis: 3_000` so exhaustion is a fast error, not a hang.
3. `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = '60s'` so a leak self-destructs.
4. `leak-detection-threshold = 60_000` (Hikari) so the leak names the stack trace at minute one, not in postmortem.

**Guardrail.** Alert on `pool.numWaiters > 0` for 60s, and on `idle in transaction` count > 0 for 60s. Both are leading indicators; the 504 is the lagging one. Add a synthetic check that hits an endpoint requiring a DB connection, so you find out in 30 seconds rather than from a customer.

**Interviewer probe:** "The database shows 4 active connections and the API is timing out. Where do you look first?" → Not the database. Look at the pool's `numWaiters`/`idleCount` and at the diff, because a queue in front of an idle database is a client-side problem.

---

## Scenario 2 — A `SELECT` on a 40M-row table suddenly goes from 30ms to 12s, with no code change

**Symptom.** The nightly finance report query (`GROUP BY customer_id, month` over `order_lines`) jumps from 30ms to 12.4s at ~02:00 each night and returns to 30ms by 02:05. No deploy. No schema change.

**Investigation.**
```sql
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, VERBOSE)
SELECT customer_id, date_trunc('month', created_at) AS m, sum(line_cents)
FROM order_lines WHERE created_at >= now() - interval '90 days' GROUP BY 1,2;
```
The plan shows a `Seq Scan` with `Heap Blocks: lossy=41234` — **a bitmap heap scan whose work_mem for the lossy bitmap exceeded the limit**. And the timing correlates with autovacuum: check `pg_stat_all_tables.last_autovacuum`, and `pg_stat_progress_vacuum`.

**Cause.** Autovacuum on `order_lines` runs at ~02:00 (it always has, but the table grew past a threshold this week). During vacuum, the visibility map is being rebuilt, so **index-only scans on this table temporarily stop being index-only** and degrade to heap fetches. The report query's plan was an index-only scan; now it does 400k random heap reads. Nothing about the query changed; the *table's VACUUMed fraction* changed.

**Fix.**
1. Confirm with `SELECT relname, n_live_tup, n_dead_tup, last_vacuum, last_autovacuum, vacuum_count, autovacuum_count FROM pg_stat_user_tables WHERE relname='order_lines';`
2. Move the report so it does not run during the vacuum: pin the maintenance window (`ALTER TABLE order_lines SET (autovacuum_vacuum_cost_delay = 0)` makes it faster; `pg_repack` in the quiet window fixes the underlying bloat), or move autovacuum to a schedule with `vacuum_cost_delay` tuned, or run the report off-peak.
3. Short-term: `VACUUM (ANALYZE) order_lines;` after the window to rebuild the visibility map.

**The lesson to state:** an index-only scan's cost depends on the **visibility map**, which is data state, not schema state. This is why "the plan did not change but the latency did" is a real category of incident, and why `EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)` (with `Heap Blocks` / `Heap Fetches`) beats reading the plan without buffers.

**Guardrail.** Alert on `pg_stat_user_tables.n_dead_tup / n_live_tup > 0.2` for any table over 100M rows, and monitor `Heap Fetches` in slow-query logging (`auto_explain` with `log_analyze`).

---

## Scenario 3 — Users report seeing another user's name and order history

**Symptom.** Seventeen support tickets in one hour: "I logged in and saw my colleague's dashboard". Not a stale-data ticket. A *data-leak* ticket. Highest severity.

**Investigation.**
1. **Stop the bleeding first:** disable the cache layer for the affected endpoint with a flag; do not investigate with users' data exposed.
2. Reproduce locally. Log the `Cache-Control`, `Vary`, and the CDN cache key for the endpoint.
3. Find the cache key construction. The bug is almost always in the key derivation, not in the eviction.

**Cause.** A refactor in the caching layer changed the key from `user:{sessionId}:dashboard` to `dashboard:{tenantId}` — dropping the *user* component while keeping the *tenant* component. Every user in a tenant shared one cache entry. The CDN was configured with `Cache-Control: public` on that endpoint, so the shared copy was served edge-side too.

**Fix.**
1. Roll back the key change.
2. Purge the affected namespace at every layer: Redis (`SCAN` + `DEL` or, better, a versioned namespace bump), the CDN (surrogate-key purge), and the app L1 (restart or broadcast).
3. Audit the log for the exposure window and follow your incident-response policy. Data that crossed users is a notification/regulatory question in many jurisdictions, not just a bug.

**The two design lessons.**
- **Cache keys are a security boundary.** Tenant and user identity must be *inside* the key by construction, not by convention. A key builder that requires a tenant argument (a typed `CacheKey` factory that takes `{tenantId, userId, resource, id}` and cannot be called without them) prevents this class of bug structurally.
- **`public` on a personalized response is a leak waiting for a CDN.** Default to `private`; opt into `public` deliberately, with a review checkpoint.

**Guardrail.** A test that asserts: for two different users in the same tenant, `cacheKey(u1, r) !== cacheKey(u2, r)`; plus a lint rule banning string-concatenated cache keys in the HTTP layer; plus a CI check that no endpoint returning a `user`-scoped body emits `Cache-Control: public`.

---

## Scenario 4 — The nightly batch "succeeds" and reports 0 rows, every night

**Symptom.** The monthly-usage rollup job logs `refreshed mv_daily_revenue in 4ms` and the dashboard shows zeros. It has been wrong for six weeks; nobody noticed because the dashboard was checked by someone with the read replica, and the refresh was writing to the primary.

**Investigation.**
```sql
SELECT schemaname, matviewname, ispopulated, last_refresh FROM pg_matviews;
-- Note: there is NO last_refresh column. That is the whole problem: Postgres does not record it.
SELECT relname, n_tup_ins, n_tup_upd, n_tup_del FROM pg_stat_user_matviews WHERE relname='mv_daily_revenue';
```

**Cause.** The job used `REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW CONCURRENTLY` inside a transaction. In PG 11 and earlier, `REFRESH ... CONCURRENTLY` cannot run inside a transaction block, so it errored; the wrapper caught the error, logged it at `debug`, and the "4ms" was the time to fail. `pg_stat_user_matviews.n_tup_ins` was zero.

**Fix.**
1. Run `REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW CONCURRENTLY` outside an explicit transaction (autocommit), and check the `UNIQUE` index exists — without it, `CONCURRENTLY` fails with `could not create unique index`.
2. **Record the refresh time yourself.** Postgres does not track it, so add it:
```sql
ALTER TABLE mv_daily_revenue ADD COLUMN refreshed_at timestamptz;
CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION refresh_mv_daily_revenue() RETURNS void LANGUAGE plpgsql AS $$
BEGIN
  REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW CONCURRENTLY mv_daily_revenue;   -- must be top-level
  EXECUTE 'UPDATE mv_daily_revenue SET refreshed_at = now()';
END $$;
```
Hmm, careful: that `EXECUTE` is a separate statement in the same transaction as nothing else, which is fine, but `REFRESH ... CONCURRENTLY` inside a function body is still inside a transaction block and will be rejected. The correct shape is a scheduled job that issues the two statements as separate autocommit commands, then records the timestamp. Know that precisely; it is a classic interview trap.

3. Alert on `max(refreshed_at)` age > 3× the refresh interval.

**Guardrail.** Every scheduled data job must (a) exit non-zero on failure, (b) record its own last-success timestamp in a table, (c) be monitored by that timestamp, not by its logs. Logs are not a control plane.

---

## Scenario 5 — `ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN` takes down production

**Symptom.** 14:22: a release job runs `ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN shipped_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now()`. Within 90 seconds, request p99 goes to 60s and the error rate hits 40%. Cancelling the migration recovers the site.

**Investigation.**
```sql
SELECT pid, state, wait_event_type, wait_event, now()-query_start AS dur, left(query,100)
FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE wait_event_type = 'Lock';
```
You would have seen the `ALTER TABLE` sitting behind a long-running `SELECT` with `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` requested, and then a queue of ordinary `SELECT`s and `INSERT`s behind it, all waiting on `Lock`.

**Cause.** Two compounding errors:
1. **No `lock_timeout`.** The DDL waited for the long-running report query to finish (or forever), and because an `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` lock request queues *ahead* of subsequent requests for the same lock mode ordering, every subsequent query on that table queued behind it. **A lock request that cannot be satisfied blocks every later request for the same lock.** That queue is the outage, and the migration was only the trigger.
2. `DEFAULT now()` is a **volatile** expression. On the version in use, that forces a full table rewrite under the lock — so the lock would have been held for the entire rewrite anyway.

**Fix (immediate).** `SELECT pg_cancel_backend(<ddl_pid>);` — cancel the head of the queue, not the report; cancelling the report does not help if the DDL already holds the queue.

**Fix (permanent).**
```sql
SET lock_timeout = '3s';                 -- every DDL, always
SET statement_timeout = '120s';
ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN shipped_at timestamptz;   -- nullable, no default: metadata only
-- backfill in batches; then
ALTER TABLE orders ALTER COLUMN shipped_at SET DEFAULT now();
```
And put `lock_timeout` in the migration runner's `SET LOCAL` so it cannot be forgotten (see Problem 8).

**Guardrail.** DDL must go through the migration runner, never from a laptop. Alert on any `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` lock wait longer than 5s. Add a pre-flight check to the release pipeline: for each `ALTER`, look up whether a rewrite is required (Postgres `pg_class.relrewrite`, or simply: is the change in the safe list?).

---

## Scenario 6 — Sessions all log users out at once, exactly hourly

**Symptom.** Every hour, on the hour, a spike of re-logins. Redis `evicted_keys` is climbing. `INFO memory` shows `used_memory` at the instance cap and `mem_fragmentation_ratio` 1.9.

**Investigation.**
```bash
redis-cli INFO memory | grep -E 'used_memory|maxmemory|fragmentation'
redis-cli CONFIG GET maxmemory maxmemory-policy
redis-cli INFO stats | grep evicted_keys
redis-cli --bigkeys                 # find the key holding the memory
redis-cli --memkeys                 # sample for large keys
```
`maxmemory` = 2 GB on a 2 GB instance (the classic mistake), policy `allkeys-lru`, and `evicted_keys` in the millions.

**Cause.** `maxmemory` was set to the full instance RAM, so Redis never got to evict before the kernel OOM risk, and more importantly the working set simply did not fit: sessions plus cache plus rate-limit counters on one instance. LRU then evicted the coldest keys — and because sessions are touched on *every* request while some cache keys are cold, the eviction pattern was correct for LRU but the *capacity* was wrong, so enough sessions got evicted to force mass logouts.

**Fix.**
1. Raise the headroom: `maxmemory` = 60-75% of instance RAM so Redis evicts before the kernel does.
2. Split the instances by data class (6.17.3): sessions on their own with `allkeys-lru` and a long TTL, cache on `allkeys-lru`, rate limits and locks on `noeviction`. Now a cache eviction storm cannot log anyone out, and a rate-limiter memory spike cannot fail cache writes.
3. Add TTLs to keys that had none (a `SCAN` for keys with no TTL is the classic leak — a `final:` marker set of keys added to `SCAN` and check `TTL`).

**Guardrail.** Alert on `evicted_keys` rate > 0 for a `noeviction` instance (must be exactly 0) and > 1% of gets for a cache instance; alert on `used_memory/maxmemory > 0.9`; track `fragmentation_ratio` and enable `activedefrag`.

---

## Scenario 7 — Read replicas lag 40 minutes, then the primary runs out of disk

**Symptom.** `pg_stat_replication.replay_lag` climbs steadily to 40 minutes. `pg_stat_replication` then shows the replica in `catchup` state and the primary's `pg_wal` directory grows to 400 GB. Two alarms: replica lag, and disk at 95%.

**Investigation.**
```sql
-- Primary
SELECT application_name, state, sync_state, sent_lsn, replay_lsn,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(sent_lsn, replay_lsn)) AS behind FROM pg_stat_replication;
SELECT * FROM pg_replication_slots WHERE slot_name = 'replica1_slot';
SELECT slot_name, active, restart_lsn, wal_status FROM pg_replication_slots;
-- Replica
SELECT now() - pg_last_xact_replay_timestamp() AS delay, pg_is_in_recovery();
-- What is the replica actually doing?
SELECT pid, state, wait_event_type, wait_event, now()-query_start, left(query,150)
FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE datname IS NOT NULL;
```

**Cause.** Two distinct bugs, both common.
1. **A long-running query on the replica.** `hot_standby_feedback = on` means the replica tells the primary "do not vacuum rows I still need". A `SELECT` running for 6 hours against a 3-day-old snapshot pins `xmin` on the primary, which blocks vacuum, which grows the WAL. That is the *disk* problem.
2. **The replica is simply slower than the primary under load.** A long analytical query saturating its I/O means replay cannot keep up. The gap grows; the replication slot holds the WAL so the data is not lost (good), but disk fills (bad).

**Fix.**
1. Kill the offending query on the replica; set `max_standby_streaming_delay = 30s` (already set) so it *would* have been cancelled — find out why it was not (probably `max_standby_streaming_delay = 0`, i.e. "never cancel", which someone set during an incident and never reverted).
2. `ALTER SYSTEM SET max_standby_streaming_delay = 30s` and restart the replica; move long reports to a separate logical-replica analytics cluster.
3. Provision standby storage like the primary's (this is the "slow disk stalls synchronous commits" failure from 6.13.3, in its lag form).
4. Set `max_slot_wal_keep_size` (e.g. `10GB`) so a hopeless replica cannot fill the primary's disk — it gets terminated and re-synced rather than taking down production.
5. Free disk now: if you have archived the WAL, you can let the slot go and re-`pg_basebackup` the replica.

**Guardrail.** Alert on: replica `replay_lag` > 60s; slot `wal_status` = `extended` or `unreserved`; `pg_wal` size; oldest `xmin` age across `pg_stat_activity`; and `max_standby_streaming_delay` being 0 (config drift check).

---

## Scenario 8 — `ERROR: could not serialize access due to read/write dependencies among transactions`

**Symptom.** Intermittent failures, roughly 1 in 200 requests, all with SQLSTATE 40001, all on the same endpoint, all under concurrency. Never in staging (which has 5 users).

**Investigation.** Find the isolation level and the two queries involved. The 40001 under `SERIALIZABLE` means the planner found a dangerous structure — a read-write dependency cycle. The classic shape is:

```sql
-- Transaction A (request 1): "check then insert"
BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE;
SELECT * FROM inventory WHERE sku = 'X' AND qty > 0;         -- read
INSERT INTO reservations (sku, user_id) VALUES ('X', $1);   -- write
COMMIT;

-- Transaction B (request 2): the same, concurrently
-- A read B's rows, B reads A's writes -> cycle -> one must abort
```

**Cause.** `SERIALIZABLE` is working exactly as designed. The code treats the transaction as retryable-free, so the abort surfaces to the user as a 500. Under `READ COMMITTED` this would instead have produced a *double reservation*, which the team would have preferred to handle with a constraint.

**Fix — two options, and you must choose deliberately.**

Option A (keep `SERIALIZABLE`, add the mandatory retry loop):
```ts
async function withSerializableRetry<T>(fn: (c: PoolClient) => Promise<T>, attempts = 5): Promise<T> {
  for (let i = 0; i < attempts; i++) {
    try {
      return await withTx(c => fn(c), 'SERIALIZABLE');
    } catch (err: any) {
      if (err.code !== '40001' && err.code !== '40P01') throw err;   // serialization OR deadlock
      const backoff = Math.min(50 * 2 ** i, 1000) * (0.5 + Math.random());  // jittered
      metrics.serializationRetries.inc();
      await sleep(backoff);
    }
  }
  throw new ConflictAfterRetries();
}
```
Option B (drop to `READ COMMITTED` and make the invariant declarative):
```sql
-- Now the database, not the isolation level, is the correctness mechanism
ALTER TABLE reservations ADD CONSTRAINT one_active_reservation_per_user_per_sku
  UNIQUE (user_id, sku) WHERE status = 'active';
```
```ts
try {
  await db.query('INSERT INTO reservations (sku, user_id) VALUES ($1,$2)', [sku, userId]);
} catch (err: any) {
  if (err.code === '23505') return { ok: false, reason: 'already_reserved' };
  throw err;
}
```

**The senior answer:** "Option B. `SERIALIZABLE` buys correctness by aborting transactions, and the retry cost plus the p99 tail is a real, recurring tax on every write path. A unique constraint buys the same correctness declaratively, with no aborts, and it is self-documenting. I reserve `SERIALIZABLE` for the handful of operations where a multi-row invariant genuinely cannot be expressed as a constraint, and those always have the retry loop."

**Guardrail.** Never ship a `SERIALIZABLE` transaction without the retry loop; add a CI check that greps for `SERIALIZABLE` and asserts a retry wrapper exists. Alert on the 40001 rate: a sudden rise means hot-row contention, and the fix is a narrower transaction, not more retries.

---

## Scenario 9 — Latency tripled after adding a trigger "for audit purposes"

**Symptom.** A `BEFORE UPDATE` trigger was added to `orders` two weeks ago. Since then, bulk updates (`UPDATE orders SET status = ...` touching 5M rows in a backfill) take 4× longer, and `pg_stat_user_tables.n_tup_upd` shows no anomaly.

**Investigation.**
```sql
SELECT tgname, pg_get_triggerdef(t.oid) FROM pg_trigger t
WHERE tgrelid = 'orders'::regclass AND NOT tgisinternal;
SELECT * FROM pg_stat_user_tables WHERE relname='orders';
-- n_tup_upd = 5,000,000 ; the trigger fired 5,000,000 times
```

**Cause.** The trigger was `FOR EACH ROW` and did a `SELECT` against another table to build an audit entry. Five million PL/pgSQL invocations, five million extra index lookups, five million writes to `audit_log` — and because it was `BEFORE` and returned `NEW`, it also blocked HOT updates (any function call in a trigger that touches the row forces a full tuple rewrite, defeating the heap-only-tuple optimization).

**Fix.**
1. Switch to a statement-level trigger with transition tables (see 6.8.3):
```sql
CREATE TRIGGER orders_audit_stmt AFTER UPDATE ON orders
REFERENCING NEW TABLE AS newtab OLD TABLE AS oldtab
FOR EACH STATEMENT EXECUTE FUNCTION audit_orders_bulk();
```
One function call, one set-based `INSERT ... SELECT`. On 5M rows this is minutes instead of hours.
2. Drop the old row trigger after the new one is verified.
3. Ensure the audit function is `SECURITY DEFINER SET search_path = pg_catalog, public` and that `audit_log` has no trigger on it.

**Guardrail.** In migration review, treat any `FOR EACH ROW` trigger that performs I/O on a table with >10M rows as a finding. Add `pg_stat_user_tables` write-rate monitoring so a trigger's write amplification is visible as a toasts/s ratio.

---

## Scenario 10 — An index exists, the plan "should" use it, and the query is still slow

**Symptom.** `SELECT ... WHERE tenant_id = 42 AND metadata @> '{"plan":"pro"}' ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 50` takes 800ms. There is a GIN index on `metadata` and a B-tree on `(tenant_id, created_at DESC)`.

**Investigation.**
```sql
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT * FROM events WHERE tenant_id = 42 AND payload @> '{"plan":"pro"}'
ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 50;
```
```
Limit  (cost=...)
  ->  Sort  (actual time=812.034..812.040 rows=50)
        Sort Method: top-N heapsort  Memory: 65kB
        ->  Bitmap Heap Scan on events  (actual time=800.1..812.0 rows=498321)
              Recheck Cond: (payload @> '{"plan":"pro"}'::jsonb)
              Rows Removed by Index Recheck: 1204
              Heap Blocks: exact=9841
              ->  Bitmap Index Scan on events_payload_idx  (actual time=12..640)
Filter: (tenant_id = 42)     <-- FILTER, not an index condition
```
Note: 498,321 rows matched the JSONB predicate and were then filtered by `tenant_id`, because there is **no index combining the two**. `tenant_id = 42` is a `Filter`, applied after the bitmap scan.

**Cause (three, and they compound).** (a) The two predicates are on different columns with separate indexes, and Postgres will happily bitmap-AND them only if both are indexable *and* the planner estimates it worthwhile — it did not combine them here because `tenant_id` had no index that the bitmap could use at that selectivity. (b) Even with a combined index, the `ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 50` needs a sort because no index provides that order after the equality+containment predicate. (c) The GIN pending list: rows inserted in the last hour are in the pending list, so the "recent" rows the sort wants most are partly invisible to the index.

**Fix, in order of value:**
1. **Promote the hot field to a typed generated column and index it:**
```sql
ALTER TABLE events ADD COLUMN plan text GENERATED ALWAYS AS (payload->>'plan') STORED;
CREATE INDEX ON events (tenant_id, plan, created_at DESC);
-- Now: tenant_id = 42 AND plan = 'pro' ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 50
-- is a single index range scan with no sort.
```
2. Or a composite GIN: `CREATE EXTENSION btree_gin; CREATE INDEX ON events USING gin (tenant_id, payload jsonb_path_ops);` so both are in one bitmap.
3. Run `VACUUM events;` if recent inserts are missing (pending list).
4. Re-measure; only then consider a materialized view for the top-N-per-tenant query, which is the shape a materialized view is genuinely for.

**The lesson to say out loud:** an index on column A does not help a query that also filters on column B unless the planner can combine them (composite index, bitmap AND, or a covering index with `INCLUDE`). And `LIMIT` with an `ORDER BY` on a different column than the index provides still needs a sort of the *entire* matching set. `pg_trgm`/`jsonb_path_ops` and promoted generated columns are the two tools that fix 90% of "the JSONB column is not queryable" problems.

---

## Scenario 11 — The rate limiter is rejecting legitimate users at 5% of the limit

**Symptom.** A dashboard shows "rate limit exceeded" for normal traffic. Instrumented actual request rate is ~5 req/s against a configured limit of 100/min. The rejection rate is ~4%.

**Investigation.**
```bash
redis-cli --scan --pattern 'rl:*' | head -50      # are all users sharing a key?
redis-cli HGETALL 'rl:{login}:abc123'
redis-cli MONITOR | head -20                        # look at the actual keys being touched
```
Every key looks like `rl:{login}:abc123` where `abc123` is the same for all users.

**Cause.** The identity came from a header the CDN was overwriting or stripping: `req.headers['x-user-id']` was `undefined` for requests that had not yet authenticated (the CDN served a cached `/dashboard` shell that then called the API without the header), so `identity = String(undefined ?? 'undefined')` collapsed the entire anonymous population into one bucket.

**Fix.**
1. Reject requests with no identity explicitly (`401`) rather than substituting a sentinel — a missing identity must never become a shared bucket.
2. Derive identity from the authenticated session at the API layer, after auth, not from a client-supplied header.
3. Hash the identity so it is not a PII key in Redis, and include the route in the key so different endpoints do not share a bucket.

**The same bug class, second instance:** `client.ip` behind a load balancer is the load balancer's IP, so all users share the `ip` bucket. Two fixes, both needed: take the real IP from `X-Forwarded-For` **only** when the immediate peer is a trusted proxy (check `req.socket.remoteAddress` against the proxy allowlist), and prefer `req.ip` as resolved by a trusted-proxy configuration in your framework (`app.set('trust proxy', ...)`). Getting this wrong is how an office of 500 people behind one NAT gets locked out by an auth rate limiter.

**Guardrail.** Per-limiter metrics of `allowed`, `limited`, and `distinct_keys_seen`; alert when `distinct_keys_seen` collapses (a single key with a high request rate is exactly this bug); and a synthetic test that asserts two different identities get two different buckets.

---

## Scenario 12 — A PITR restore promoted at the wrong moment

**Symptom.** During a quarterly drill, the restore ran for 40 minutes and then promoted, but the recovered database was 90 seconds *newer* than the intended target. The engineer assumes the target was wrong.

**Investigation.**
```bash
grep recovery_target "$PGDATA/postgresql.auto.conf"
# recovery_target_time = '2024-06-01 14:36:59+00'
# recovery_target_action = promote        <-- the default since PG 12
tail -50 $PGDATA/log/*.log
# LOG:  recovery stopping after WAL location (LSN) "0/2A9F4C50"
# LOG:  selected new timeline ID: 2
# LOG:  database system is ready to accept connections
```
The log line `recovery stopping after WAL location ...` proves the target **was** reached. So the "90 seconds newer" reading is not a restore failure; it is the **inclusive-boundary semantics plus transaction commit timing**: `recovery_target_inclusive = on` includes the transaction that was in progress at the target, and a transaction that *started* before 14:36:59 and *committed* at 14:37:31 is replayed in full if the planner's chosen stopping point is the first record at or after the target. Wall-clock target matching compares WAL record timestamps, not commit timestamps, so a transaction spanning the boundary lands entirely on one side.

**Fix / correct procedure.**
1. **Use `recovery_target_lsn` or a named restore point instead of a timestamp** whenever precision matters:
```bash
# Before the incident, record it; or create a point deliberately
psql -c "SELECT pg_create_restore_point('before_incident')"
psql -c "SELECT pg_current_wal_lsn()"          # the LSN to record in monitoring
# At restore time:
recovery_target_lsn = '0/2A9F4C48'
recovery_target_inclusive = off                # stop BEFORE that record
recovery_target_action = pause                 # verify before promoting
```
2. **Always `recovery_target_action = pause`**, then check `pg_last_wal_replay_lsn()` against the expected LSN from monitoring, then `SELECT pg_wal_replay_resume();` and promote deliberately. This is the guardrail that converts a silent wrong-point restore into a caught one.
3. Set `recovery_target_timeline = 'latest'` when recovering along a failover timeline, and be aware it accepts a timeline that an operator created by accident.
4. If `restore_command` cannot find a segment in the middle of the range, replay stops and — with `promote` — you land at the *end* of what was available. That is the same class of failure and the same `pause`-then-verify guardrail catches it.

**Guardrail.** Record `pg_current_wal_lsn()` every 10 seconds in monitoring so the target is a lookup rather than an investigation. Put `recovery_target_action = pause` in the runbook template so it cannot be omitted. And diff row counts for the affected tables between the restored copy and the expected values from the incident timestamp — a 90-second discrepancy in a high-write table is expected, and that expectation should be written down *before* the drill.

---

# 6.8-6.17 INTERVIEW Q&A (40 questions with model answers)

🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

Answers are written as you would say them: the decision, then the reasoning, then the trade-off, then the failure scenario. Numbers appear because "it depends" without numbers is not an answer.

---

**Q1. What is a view, and when does it make a query slower?**
A view is a stored `SELECT` that the planner expands at each reference; it holds no data. It never makes a query slower *per se* — the plan is the same — but it makes two things harder: debugging, because the plan shows the spliced-in definition rather than the name you typed, and evolution, because a view is a contract other code depends on. The place it genuinely hurts is when people build a stack of ten views, each joining, and then wonder why a `pg_stat_statements` entry is opaque. It also does not reduce round trips: an ORM that queries per view row is an N+1 problem wearing a view as a disguise. Use a view for reuse and for a permissions surface (`security_barrier`), not as a performance device.

**Q2. `security_barrier` view: what problem does it solve and why does it exist?**
Postgres pushes predicates below user-supplied functions inside a view, so a security predicate on the view can end up applied *after* a function that sees the row. A security barrier prevents that reordering for the view's own quals. It matters when the view is your row-level isolation mechanism and the underlying tables contain rows the calling role should never see. Realistically I pair it with `SECURITY INVOKER` and grants on base tables, and use row-level security (6.6) as the primary mechanism; a barrier view is the secondary one.

**Q3. When would you use a materialized view instead of adding an index?**
When the query is an aggregation over millions of rows and the result is read far more often than the underlying data changes. An index helps *finding* rows; it does not precompute a `GROUP BY` over them, so a dashboard query aggregating 200M rows stays expensive even with a perfect index. The tell is `EXPLAIN` showing a large `HashAggregate` or `Sort` node over many pages. I also use a matview when the query joins several large tables in a shape that would need three indexes. The trade-offs: staleness bounded by the refresh interval, full-refresh cost, and the need for a unique index if you want `REFRESH CONCURRENTLY`. Before reaching for it, I check `pg_matviews` for existing ones and consider a covering index plus an incremental rollup table maintained by the write path.

**Q4. `REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW CONCURRENTLY`: what are the requirements and the failure modes?**
Requirements: a `UNIQUE` index on the view, it cannot run inside a transaction block, and (depending on version) it cannot be run on a view with certain column defaults. Failure modes: without the unique index it fails outright; if `archive_command`/WAL is misconfigured the refresh can be cancelled; and the biggest one operationally — if it is wrapped in a transaction by an ORM, it errors and, if the error is swallowed, the view silently goes stale forever. Use `recovery_target_action`-style discipline here too: record the refresh time in your own column and alert on its age, because Postgres does not track `last_refresh`.

**Q5. When are triggers a bad idea?**
When a declarative constraint would do (the rule becomes invisible to readers and tooling); when the trigger performs I/O or a network call; when it is `FOR EACH ROW` on a table with bulk updates (it is per-row interpreter overhead and it blocks HOT updates); when it maintains a counter that the write path could maintain with a guarded `UPDATE`; and when it encodes business logic that the team cannot test or review. The good uses are `updated_at`, audit logging the application cannot bypass, cross-row invariants no `CHECK` can express, and deferred constraint triggers.

**Q6. What is the trap with session-level advisory locks?**
They are tied to the *connection*, not the transaction. With a connection pool, a session-level lock is acquired by one request's connection and released by whichever request later borrows that connection, or never. Also they do not survive a transaction boundary, so you cannot use them for "hold the lock until the work is done" if "the work" spans commits. Use `pg_advisory_xact_lock` (or `pg_try_advisory_xact_lock`) which releases at commit/rollback and cannot leak. If you need session scope, you must pin a dedicated connection, which is usually the signal that you want a different mechanism.

**Q7. Stored procedure or application code? Where is the line?**
Database when the logic must be atomic with the data, must be shared by all writers including ETL and ad-hoc SQL, or is set-based and dramatically faster there. Application when it calls an external system, changes weekly, needs to be unit-tested and traced, or is presentation logic. Concretely: constraints and derived totals in the schema, small `STABLE` SQL functions for reuse, set-based procedures for batch jobs, and everything else in the app. Never a network call inside a procedure or a transaction.

**Q8. Why is a PL/pgSQL loop so slow compared to a single set-based statement?**
Three compounding costs: each iteration is a PL/pgSQL interpreter invocation with its own context; each statement inside the loop is planned and executed separately; and per-row operations touch the index and heap row by row, generating far more WAL than a set-based rewrite. The set-based version also lets the planner choose a join strategy, use a sort or hash aggregate, and process in a single pass over sorted input. The rule: if you can write it as one statement with `INSERT ... SELECT`, `UPDATE ... FROM`, or a window function, do, even if it looks less readable.

**Q9. `IMMUTABLE` vs `STABLE` vs `VOLATILE`, and what breaks when you get it wrong?**
`IMMUTABLE` means same input always gives same output and no side effects, so it can be constant-folded, inlined, and indexed in expression indexes. `STABLE` means same output within a statement and may read tables. `VOLATILE` may change per call. Getting it wrong is silent: a function marked `IMMUTABLE` that reads a table produces a wrong answer the planner caches; a `STABLE` function that reads `now()` produces one evaluation reused across the whole query, so a per-row time value is wrong; a pure function marked `VOLATILE` loses inlining and blocks index usage. Rule: `VOLATILE` is the safe default; only claim less after you are sure.

**Q10. How many database connections should a service have?**
Derived, not guessed: concurrency = throughput × hold-time. At 2,000 req/s with a 20 ms hold time you need 40 connections of real work, and queueing theory says going far past useful concurrency reduces throughput. So: a small pool per instance (10 to 20 is normal), with many client connections multiplexed onto few server connections by a transaction-mode proxy. The database should see tens to low hundreds of server connections, not thousands. Budget: `pool_size × replicas ≤ proxy.default_pool_size`, and `max_connections` must leave headroom for humans, replication slots, and background workers.

**Q11. Why can a smaller pool be faster than a large one?**
Because throughput is not concurrency. Past a point, additional concurrent connections contend on shared buffer manager locks, buffer pins, and relation locks, and each query spends more time waiting than computing. The latency of the *waiting* grows superlinearly while completed work per second plateaus and then declines. This is why the classic failure is "we increased the pool to fix timeouts and made it worse": the timeouts were a symptom of contention, not of pool size.

**Q12. PgBouncer: what does transaction mode break?**
Anything that assumes it owns a backend for the duration of a *session*: `SET` outside a transaction (set it in the connection string `options` instead), session-level advisory locks, `LISTEN`/`NOTIFY`, `WITH HOLD` cursors, and session-scoped `PREPARE` statements (fixed with `max_prepared_statements`, otherwise the driver must use simple protocol or unnamed statements). Also: PgBouncer in transaction mode is only safe if your application does not rely on `SET TRANSACTION` state persisting across the pool boundary. Everything above fails with an error rather than silently, which is the good case.

**Q13. What is `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` for and why is it the most valuable Postgres setting?**
It kills connections that opened a transaction and then went idle — leaked transactions from a missing rollback. Those transactions hold an open snapshot (blocking vacuum on the primary, which causes bloat and eventually wraparound pressure) and can hold row locks. A 60-second timeout makes a leak self-healing instead of cumulative. Combined with `statement_timeout` and `lock_timeout`, it converts three classes of bug from "outage" to "error".

**Q14. How do you diagnose pool exhaustion?**
Three questions, in order. (1) Is the database saturated or is the pool the queue? Compare pool `numWaiters` and `idleCount` against database `active` connections. High waiters + idle database = leak. High waiters + saturated database = genuinely slow queries, fix the query. (2) Which connections are long-held? `pg_stat_activity` ordered by `xact_start` age, and `state='idle in transaction'` as the leak signature. (3) What changed? Diff the deploy; grep for `pool.connect()` without a matching `finally`. The fix for a leak is a `finally`, a `connectionTimeoutMillis`, and `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout`.

**Q15. When would you use `jsonb` and when a real column?**
`jsonb` when the attributes are unbounded in number, rarely queried, and read as a whole document (third-party payloads, feature flags, form answers, event envelopes). A typed column when the field is filtered, sorted, joined, aggregated, constrained, or referenced by a foreign key. And the escape hatch: a `STORED` generated column plus an index on the extracted value gives you JSONB flexibility with column speed. The honest framing is that `jsonb` is a good answer to "we do not know yet" and a bad answer to "we do not want to write a migration".

**Q16. `@>` versus `->>` in `jsonb`: which one can use an index?**
`@>` (containment), `?` (key exists), `?|`, `?&` are GIN-indexable. `->>` is an extraction function; the only way to index it is a B-tree expression index or a promoted generated column. So `payload @> '{"plan":"pro"}'` uses the GIN index; `payload->>'plan' = 'pro'` does a sequential scan unless you have `CREATE INDEX ON events ((payload->>'plan'))`. In practice I promote the filtered fields to generated columns and keep `jsonb_path_ops` GIN for the containment queries I cannot enumerate.

**Q17. What is the GIN pending list and when does it bite?**
New entries go into a small pending list (bounded by `gin_pending_list_limit`, default 4 MB) and are merged into the main index by autovacuum. Until then, a search using that index can miss rows that were just inserted or updated. Symptom: "the search does not find the record I just created". Fix: `VACUUM`, or wait for autovacuum. It also means a freshly bulk-loaded table's search index is effectively empty, which matters for reindex-style operations.

**Q18. `websearch_to_tsquery` versus `to_tsquery`: why does it matter?**
`to_tsquery` accepts the raw tsquery operator syntax (`&`, `|`, `!`, `<->`, parentheses). Untrusted input in that syntax either errors (unhandled 500s) or expresses something expensive or unintended. `websearch_to_tsquery` accepts free text with `"quoted phrases"` and `-negation` and always produces a valid query or an empty one, never an error. Rule: never build a `tsquery` by concatenation from user input. If you need a query builder, parse into an AST and validate operators against an allowlist.

**Q19. Full-text search in Postgres versus Elasticsearch — how do you decide?**
Postgres FTS is enough when the corpus is under ~10M documents, QPS is modest, you want one transaction and one backup, and the search is one entity type with SQL-expressible facets. Elasticsearch earns its cost when you need real typo tolerance with analyzers beyond `pg_trgm`, per-market dictionaries and synonym files, high concurrent search QPS with fan-out, or relevance tuning that has outgrown `ts_rank`. The cost of leaving Postgres is a second source of truth, an ingestion pipeline, an NRT lag, and a full reindex for mapping changes. Note that Postgres also does vector search (`pgvector`) and can hybrid-fuse both with reciprocal rank fusion, which removes the "we need Elasticsearch for semantic search" argument for many teams.

**Q20. Arrays: when is an array the wrong choice?**
When it grows without bound (a 10,000-element "recent activity" array is a whole-row rewrite on every append, and TOASTed); when elements need their own metadata; when you need referential integrity (Postgres cannot FK an array element); when you need to query from the other side (find all posts tagged `sql`); and when another service must modify elements concurrently. Arrays are right for small, bounded, set-queried attributes: tags, a permission `int[]`, feature flags — where `&&` and `@>` with a GIN index are the access pattern.

**Q21. Sync vs async replication, and the write-path cost?**
Sync (`synchronous_standby_names`) adds one network round trip to every commit (or more, with a quorum), and if no standby is available it blocks commits entirely unless you allow zero standbys. Async costs nothing on the write path but has an RPO measured in the last seconds of WAL. I pick per data class: sync for payments and inventory, async for everything else, with `synchronous_commit` settable per session so a low-criticality service can opt out. The failure mode to name: synchronous replication makes the standby's disk speed a write-latency dependency of the primary, so standby hardware must be provisioned like the primary's.

**Q22. What makes a replica read unsafe?**
Staleness. Immediately after a write, a read from a replica may not see it (read-your-writes violated); during a lag spike the window is unbounded. Mitigations: route the writing session to the primary for N seconds (sticky routing), or use `WAIT n` after the write to make it visible on a replica, or pin the session to a replica (`CLIENT TRACKING`) for monotonic reads. Also the mechanics: `hot_standby_feedback` prevents vacuum conflicts but a long replica query then blocks primary vacuum; and `max_standby_streaming_delay` cancels long replica queries unless it is set to 0, which someone always sets "temporarily" during an incident.

**Q23. How do you prevent split brain?**
A consensus layer with a majority lease (etcd/consul via Patroni) plus real fencing. The lease prevents two healthy primaries; fencing (STONITH, or a cloud API that detaches the failed instance's storage) prevents a partitioned-but-alive old primary from accepting writes. The test is not "does failover work" but "does the system refuse to promote when the DCS loses quorum" and "can the old primary be killed automatically". Monitoring and heartbeats are not enough; an unfenced network partition is the scenario that produces two writers.

**Q24. Why is replication not a backup?**
Because a committed mistake replicates. A `DROP TABLE` in autocommit, a bad `DELETE` without a `WHERE`, an `UPDATE` that zeroes a column: all of these are ordinary WAL, so they reach every standby in milliseconds. A backup is a point-in-time copy plus the WAL archive that lets you stop replay at a moment *before* the mistake. Additionally, replication gives you no protection against a region loss, a corrupted file, or a credential compromise. So: replication for availability, backups for durability, and a restore drill to prove the backups.

**Q25. Explain PITR end to end.**
A base backup at T0 plus continuous WAL archiving. To recover to T1, provision a new instance, restore the base backup, provide a `restore_command` that fetches WAL segments from the archive, set a recovery target (time, LSN, transaction id, or a named restore point), start, and let replay run to the target. Then promote, verify invariants, and decide what to do about the writes that happened after T1. Key points: `recovery_target_action = pause` then verify the replay LSN before promoting (otherwise a target that is never reached silently promotes at the end of the archive); LSN or a named restore point beats a timestamp because wall-clock matching is timezone- and boundary-sensitive; and a missing WAL segment in the middle of the range stops replay at the wrong place.

**Q26. Why do restore drills find things every time?**
Because the restore path has dependencies the backup does not: extensions installed on the production instance, roles and grants, `postgresql.conf` settings (especially `archive_command` credentials), sequences (which after a PITR are *behind* the data, so the first insert hits a duplicate key), disk sizing for the replay, and enough privileges to read the archive bucket and the KMS key. Every one of these is invisible until you restore. So the drill is not optional, and its output (a measured RTO) is the number your availability SLO depends on.

**Q27. Partition or shard?**
Partition when the problem is retention (deleting 30% of a table monthly), query pruning on a time-bounded access pattern, or index maintenance on a very large table. Shard when you exceed one machine's CPU, RAM, or disk I/O. Partition first: it is reversible, cheap, and buys months. And note that partitioning makes unfiltered queries *slower*, so measure that the queries you have actually prune.

**Q28. What is the most common mistake in a partitioned table?**
The unique constraint. Every unique index must include the partition key, so `UNIQUE (tenant_id, slug)` is impossible on a `RANGE (created_at)` partitioned table. The workaround (a separate global unique index table, or a hash sub-key in the unique constraint) is a design decision you must make *before* partitioning, not after. The second most common is the default/`MAXVALUE` partition: if rows land there, adding the next month requires scanning and moving it under a lock.

**Q29. How do you avoid a giant tenant becoming a hot shard?**
Directory-based placement: a table mapping tenant to shard(s), so you can split one tenant across shards by time or by id range. The alternative (pure consistent hashing) cannot split one key, and a single hot key is the hardest problem in sharding — it is fixed with a shard suffix on the key plus a fan-out read, or by precomputing. The general rule to state: design for the ability to move one tenant, and validate with the real distribution rather than assuming uniformity.

**Q30. Walk me through a zero-downtime `NOT NULL` addition on a 500M-row table.**
(1) `lock_timeout = 3s`. (2) Add the column nullable with a default so new rows are always populated — metadata only. (3) Backfill the nulls in batches with `COMMIT` per batch and a sleep, idempotent (`WHERE col IS NULL`), with a kill switch and lag monitoring. (4) `ADD CONSTRAINT col_nn CHECK (col IS NOT NULL) NOT VALID` — brief lock, no scan. (5) `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` — online, `SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE`. (6) `ALTER COLUMN SET NOT NULL` — instant in PG 12+ because it trusts the validated check. (7) `DROP CONSTRAINT`. Three deploys, no table rewrite, no long lock, and a code rollback is always possible.

**Q31. Why not just write a down migration?**
Because during a rolling deploy and after a code rollback the fleet is mixed-version and the code can go back. A down migration executed automatically would drop data that the restored code expects, and executing it manually under pressure is how a recoverable mistake becomes an incident. The model is: schema changes are forward-only and additive; rollback is a code rollback; cleanup is a later forward migration. Down migrations exist only for local development.

**Q32. When is caching a correctness problem rather than a performance one?**
When a stale read can authorize an action (entitlements, permissions, feature gating for a paid feature, pricing at checkout, fraud signals) or when acting on it is irreversible (an emailed invoice, a shipped label, a charged card). Then a plain TTL is not enough: you need event-driven invalidation plus a TTL as the backstop, or versioned keys, or a read from the authority. The interview test I use: can I state, for every cached entry, the maximum staleness, the invalidation trigger, the failure behaviour when the cache is down, and the blast radius if the invalidation is missed? If not, do not add the cache.

**Q33. Cache-aside, write-through, write-back: choose one and defend it.**
Cache-aside for almost everything: the store stays authoritative, the cache is disposable and rebuildable, and the cost is a possible miss and an invalidation obligation. Write-through when the read:write ratio is extreme and a read must never touch the store — with the acknowledgement that it introduces a dual write with no shared transaction, so you have chosen *where* staleness and partial failure live. Write-back only for values whose spec already says they are approximate (view counters, "last seen"), because it makes the cache the sole copy until flushed.
