And write-around for high-cardinality one-shot reads so a scan does not evict the hot set.

**Q34. How do you prevent a cache stampede?**
Four layers, in order of value: single-flight in-process (collapses concurrent misses to one load — the cheapest and most effective); stale-while-revalidate with a hard `staleUntil` ceiling (no reader blocks after the first fill, and a persistently failing refresh does not serve day-old data forever); a distributed refresh lock with a token and a TTL longer than the compute; and TTL jitter (±20%) so keys do not expire together. Plus early recomputation (XFetch) or a background warmer for keys you know are hot, and local L1 caches so a Redis restart is not a global cold start.

**Q35. Is a Redis lock safe? How would you make it safe?**
Safe for mutual exclusion while the lease holds and no process stalls. Not safe against pauses: a process can stall (GC, container throttle, disk) past its lease, another acquires, and the paused process resumes and acts with stale authority. That is the published criticism of Redlock. Make it safe with (a) a fencing token: a monotonic counter incremented on acquisition, passed to every write to the protected resource, which rejects any token lower than the highest it has accepted; (b) a renewal loop so a long critical section keeps its lease; and (c) an idempotent critical section, which is the real fix — if running the job twice is harmless, you do not need the lock, you need an idempotency key. And if you already have a database, `pg_advisory_xact_lock` or a unique constraint is strictly better.

**Q36. Why must a lock release check the token?**
Because `DEL` is unconditional. Process A acquires, stalls, its lease expires, B acquires and releases, then A finishes and deletes B's lock — so a third process enters while B is still in the critical section. Compare-and-delete in a single Lua script (`if GET(key) == token then DEL(key) end`) makes release conditional on still owning the lock, atomically. The same reasoning applies to renewal: extend only if the token still matches, otherwise you extend someone else's lease.

**Q37. Token bucket versus sliding window — when each?**
Token bucket for user-facing API limits: it allows a defined burst (`capacity`) and enforces a sustained average (`rate`), which matches real client behaviour (batching, retries, page loads). Sliding window log for exactness when the limit is small and the abuse is high-stakes (login attempts: exact, and memory is small because the limit is small). Sliding window counter for high-volume general limiting where O(1) memory matters and a slightly approximate boundary is acceptable. Leaky bucket when you need to control the *output* rate to a fragile downstream.

**Q38. Fail open or fail closed when the rate limiter is down?**
Per endpoint, deliberately. Fail **closed** for authentication, password reset, MFA, and payment initiation — being permissive during an attack is worse than being unavailable. Fail **open** for reads, so a Redis outage does not become a total outage; pair it with load shedding so the database is not flooded. Either way it is a config flag with a metric and an alert, because a stuck fail-open flag during an incident is expensive. And keep a local in-process limiter as a floor so the Redis outage cannot mean unbounded traffic.

**Q39. Why is a cache hit not free?**
Because it is a network hop: 0.2 ms in-region, ~2 ms cross-AZ, ~30 ms cross-region. A cross-region cache hit can be slower than the indexed query it replaced. And the memory that makes the hit possible comes from somewhere: a cache large enough to be useful competes with the buffer pool and with the connection pool. So the correct question is not "is it faster" but "does this entry's hit rate justify the memory and the hop, given that the miss path is fast anyway because of an index".

**Q40. Describe multi-layer cache coherence and how you make it correct.**
Layers: browser, CDN, reverse proxy, application L1, Redis, read replicas, origin. Correctness comes from three mechanisms working together. (1) **Every layer has a TTL**, because every invalidation message can be lost; the TTL is the guarantee and invalidation is an optimization. (2) **Invalidation is event-driven and transactional**: the write and an outbox row commit together, and a relay invalidates the inner layers (Redis), then purges the outer ones (CDN surrogate keys), in that order — purging the CDN first causes an origin read that repopulates Redis from stale data. (3) **Versioned keys for correctness-critical entries**, which removes the lost-message failure mode entirely: a write bumps a generation counter and old entries become unreachable and expire on their own. Plus the ordering rule that the harder a layer is to invalidate, the shorter its TTL, and the rule that you *measure* staleness (`now - updated_at` in the response, alerting on p99) rather than asserting it.

---

# 6.8-6.17 RAPID-FIRE (60)

⚫ Expert · **Must-know**

One-line answers. If you cannot produce the right one in five seconds, go back to the section.

| # | Question | Answer |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | What does a view store? | A parse tree, not data; it is re-planned and expanded on every reference |
| 2 | What is a `security_barrier` view for? | Preventing predicate reordering below user-supplied functions, so security quals are not bypassed |
| 3 | Does a view reduce round trips? | No; an ORM querying per view row is an N+1 in disguise |
| 4 | Requirement for `REFRESH ... CONCURRENTLY`? | A `UNIQUE` index, and it cannot run inside a transaction block |
| 5 | Does Postgres track a matview's last refresh time? | No — record it yourself and alert on its age |
| 6 | What lock does a plain `REFRESH` take? | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` on the matview |
| 7 | Trigger that fires once per statement with set access? | `FOR EACH STATEMENT` with `REFERENCING NEW TABLE AS newtab` |
| 8 | What does a `BEFORE ROW` trigger returning `NULL` do? | Silently skips the row |
| 9 | When do `AFTER` trigger blocks fire? | At end of statement |
| 10 | `security_path` pitfall for `SECURITY DEFINER`? | Unpinned `search_path` allows object shadowing; use `SET search_path = pg_catalog, public` |
| 11 | Do triggers fire on `TRUNCATE`? | Only if you declare `AFTER TRUNCATE`; they are statement-level |
| 12 | Trigger firing order in Postgres? | Alphabetical by trigger name — name them `01_`, `02_` |
| 13 | Why are row triggers slow on bulk updates? | Per-row interpreter overhead plus per-statement planning; also they block HOT updates |
| 14 | What is a deferred constraint trigger for? | Cross-row invariants checked at commit rather than per row |
| 15 | Can a procedure `COMMIT`? | Yes; a function cannot |
| 16 | Consequence of a committing procedure? | It cannot run inside your ORM transaction, and partial failure leaves partial state |
| 17 | What does `IMMUTABLE` enable? | Constant folding, inlining, and use in expression indexes |
| 18 | A `STABLE` function that reads `now()`? | One evaluation reused for the whole query — a silent wrong answer |
| 19 | PL/pgSQL exception block behaviour? | Rolls back to a savepoint; the whole block's work is undone |
| 20 | Postgres advisory lock that cannot leak? | `pg_advisory_xact_lock` — releases at commit/rollback |
| 21 | Little's Law for pool sizing? | Concurrency = throughput × hold-time, plus buffer for bursts and maintenance |
| 22 | Why is a big pool slower? | Lock contention on shared buffers and relations; throughput plateaus then falls |
| 23 | `pool_size × replicas` must be ≤ ? | The proxy's `default_pool_size` per (db, user) pool |
| 24 | The leak backstop setting? | `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout`, plus `connectionTimeoutMillis` in the pool |
| 25 | Signature of a leak? | Pool waiters climbing while the database is idle; `idle in transaction` rows with an old `state_change` |
| 26 | PgBouncer's three modes? | `session`, `transaction`, `statement` |
| 27 | Default PgBouncer mode for stateless web apps? | `transaction` |
| 28 | What breaks in transaction mode? | `SET` outside a transaction, session advisory locks, `LISTEN/NOTIFY`, session `PREPARE` |
| 29 | Fix for prepared statements behind PgBouncer? | `max_prepared_statements > 0` (PgBouncer 1.21+), or simple protocol |
| 30 | PgBouncer recovery after failover? | `server_lifetime` plus `server_check_query`/`server_check_delay`, or a DNS-aware proxy |
| 31 | `maxmemory` as a fraction of RAM? | 60–75%, so Redis evicts before the kernel OOM-kills |
| 32 | `json` versus `jsonb`? | `json` stores exact text; `jsonb` is parsed, deduped, order-normalized, and indexable |
| 33 | GIN-indexable `jsonb` operators? | `@>`, `?`, `?|`, `?&` |
| 34 | `jsonb_path_ops` trade-off? | Much smaller index; only `@>` and `@?`, no key-exists |
| 35 | Making a JSONB field fast to filter? | A `STORED` generated column (`payload->>'x'`) plus a B-tree index |
| 36 | GIN pending list? | New entries sit in a small list until autovacuum merges them; recent rows can be invisible to search |
| 37 | Safe user-input search function? | `websearch_to_tsquery` or `plainto_tsquery` — never `to_tsquery` on raw input |
| 38 | `tsvector` weights order? | A (title) > B (body) > C > D |
| 39 | `ts_rank` normalization 32? | `rank/(rank+1)`, making scores comparable across queries |
| 40 | Postgres vs MySQL default isolation? | `READ COMMITTED` vs `REPEATABLE READ` (with gap locks) |
| 41 | Is MySQL DDL transactional? | No — each DDL implicitly commits |
| 42 | Dangerous MySQL clause? | `INSERT IGNORE` swallows truncation and other errors, not just duplicates |
| 43 | Postgres-only portability blockers? | `RETURNING` (no MySQL equivalent), plus arrays, ranges, and `EXCLUDE` |
| 44 | MySQL's replacement for `RETURNING`? | `LAST_INSERT_ID()`, which is connection-scoped and therefore pool-hostile |
| 45 | MySQL advisory lock's flaw? | `GET_LOCK` is session-only, has no transaction variant, and leaks through a pool |
| 46 | `synchronous_standby_names` with no standby connected? | Commits block indefinitely unless you allow zero standbys (`ANY 0 (...)`) or set `synchronous_commit = local` |
| 47 | Cost of synchronous replication? | One RTT per commit, and standby disk speed becomes a write-latency dependency |
| 48 | `synchronous_commit = remote_apply` means? | Wait until the standby applies it; zero loss, but the replica may be promoted mid-transaction |
| 49 | Right lag metrics? | `pg_wal_lsn_diff(sent_lsn, replay_lsn)` plus `pg_last_xact_replay_timestamp()` |
| 50 | `hot_standby_feedback` side effect? | A long replica query blocks primary vacuum, causing bloat |
| 51 | Split-brain prevention? | Consensus lease with a majority, plus real fencing (STONITH / storage detach) |
| 52 | Why is replication not a backup? | A committed `DROP TABLE` replicates in milliseconds |
| 53 | `archive_timeout` for? | Bounding PITR granularity during low-write periods |
| 54 | Safe recovery-target setting? | `recovery_target_action = pause`, verify the LSN, then resume — otherwise a missed target silently promotes at the archive's end |
| 55 | Pitfall after a PITR restore? | Sequences are behind the data; the first insert hits a duplicate key — `setval` from `max(id)` |
| 56 | Partition pruning requires? | The partition key in a `WHERE` clause the planner can constant-fold; `date_trunc(col) = x` prunes nothing |
| 57 | Unique constraint on a range-partitioned table? | It must include the partition key |
| 58 | `DROP PARTITION` versus `DELETE`? | `DROP` is a metadata operation; `DELETE` is a data operation (WAL, bloat, no disk shrink) |
| 59 | DDL belt-and-braces? | `SET lock_timeout = '3s'` — a blocked `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` request blocks every later request for that lock |
| 60 | Fastest online path to `NOT NULL`? | `CHECK (c IS NOT NULL) NOT VALID` → backfill → `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` → `SET NOT NULL` → drop the check |

# PHASE 6, PART 2: NoSQL, Data Access, Search, Caching, and Data Modeling

**Prerequisite note.** Sections 6.1 through 6.17 cover SQL foundations: relational modelling, normalization, joins, subqueries, window functions, transactions and isolation levels, locking and deadlocks, B-tree indexes, and query plans, plus Postgres- and MySQL-specific behaviour. This part assumes all of that and cross-references it instead of re-teaching it. The moment a question turns into "how do I make this join fast" or "what does READ COMMITTED actually permit", the answer lives in 6.1–6.17, not here. What follows is the world *around* the relational core: the non-relational stores, the caching and rate-limiting layers, search, ORMs, and the modelling practice that ties them together.

The one-sentence version of this phase: **pick the store from the access patterns, not from the marketing, and treat every denormalized copy as a liability you now owe an invalidation strategy.**

---

# 6.18 NoSQL Taxonomy and the Decision Framework
Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition:** A taxonomy of non-relational storage shapes, plus a decision procedure for choosing among them, and an honest account of what consistency actually costs.

**Why this topic is an interview favourite:** almost every "design a system" question is really asking "which store, and what consistency are you paying for". Candidates who say "NoSQL scales better" or "MongoDB is schemaless" fail on the spot. Candidates who can say "the requirement is a *bounded, known set of access patterns* over a *wide* keyspace, and I accept eventual consistency on cross-document reads, so a wide-column store fits; the account balance stays in Postgres because it needs a serializable transfer" are hired.

## 6.18.1 The families

| Family | Data unit | Query model | Typical engine | Sweet spot | Pain point |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Document | Self-contained JSON/BSON tree, accessed by id or by index predicates | Rich predicate matching, aggregation pipelines, ad-hoc-ish field queries | MongoDB, Couchbase, Postgres `jsonb`, Firestore | Semi-structured entities, one entity per read, fast iteration on shape | Joins are emulated, 16MB ceiling, document = write-amplification unit, no cross-document constraints without transactions |
| Key-value | Opaque value under a key | Get / put / delete / scan-by-prefix | Redis, DynamoDB, Memcached, etcd | Caching, sessions, counters, rate limits, idempotency, locks, queues | You choose the encoding; there is no secondary index unless you build one |
| Wide-column | Row-family inside a partition, addressed by partition key + clustering range | "Read this partition, scan this clustering range" | Cassandra, HBase, Bigtable, ScyllaDB | Very high write ingest, known query shapes, time-ordered data, multi-DC | No joins, no ad-hoc predicates, one table per query, painful schema evolution |
| Graph | Nodes, typed edges, properties | Variable-depth traversal, path finding, neighbourhood | Neo4j, Neptune, ArangoDB, RedisGraph, Cosmos DB Gremlin | Relationship-centric domains: fraud rings, org charts, dependencies, recommendations, identity | Traversal cost explodes; no join optimizer; awkward for plain CRUD and reporting |
| Time-series | Timestamp-keyed measurements | Time-range scan, rollups, retention | TimescaleDB, InfluxDB, Prometheus TSDB, ClickHouse | Metrics, IoT, events, anything append-mostly with time-window reads | Retention and cardinality, not transactions or relations |
| Vector | High-dimensional embeddings + ANN index | Approximate nearest-neighbour top-k, often filtered | pgvector, Milvus, Qdrant, Weaviate, Pinecone | Semantic search, dedupe, recommendations, RAG retrieval | Recall vs latency vs build cost, filtering inside ANN, re-index on model change |
| Search/analytical | Inverted index / columnar segments | Full-text relevance, aggregations, OLAP rollups | Elasticsearch/OpenSearch, ClickHouse, BigQuery, Snowflake | Relevance-ranked retrieval, faceted navigation, analytics | Near-real-time lag, mapping/index lifecycle cost, source-of-truth duplication |

Note the overlap: Postgres alone can play document (JSONB + GIN), time-series (partitioning + BRIN), vector (pgvector), and search (tsvector + GIN + a materialized view) roles, often more cheaply than a fleet of purpose-built services. A separate engine earns its keep when one of these becomes the *dominant* workload at scale, not merely because it is possible.

## 6.18.2 CAP, without the slogan

The textbook claim "pick two of C, A, P" is wrong in a way interviewers check for:

- **If your system is distributed across a network, partition tolerance is not a choice.** Network partitions are not an exotic failure; they are routine (a switch reboot, a NAT timeout, a rolling deploy, a misrouted packet). Every distributed system must keep operating during a partition, so every distributed system is P. What you actually choose is the *behaviour during* a partition.
- **"C" is per-operation, not per-database.** A system can offer linearizable reads on one key, eventual reads on another, and asynchronous replication to the warehouse. Consistency is a property of an operation against a set of replicas, and a good answer quantifies it per endpoint.
- **"A" is rarely a free choice either.** Availability during a partition means two replicas may accept conflicting writes, so you need reconciliation: last-write-wins (with clock skew as a real hazard), vector clocks or hybrid logical clocks, CRDTs, or a quorum that makes divergence bounded rather than impossible.
- **The practical axis is latency vs consistency, not C vs A.** "CAP is a theorem, PACELC is the useful one" is the interview-grade version.

**The honest framing to give out loud:** "I assume partitions, so I trade freshness for availability, and the interesting engineering is bounding how stale things get and making the convergence correct."

## 6.18.3 PACELC

> **P**artition tolerance: **A**vailability or **C**onsistency. **E**lse (no partition): **L**atency or **C**onsistency.

| Workload | Partitioned | Healthy | Typical implementation |
|---|---|---|---|
| Shopping cart | Available, last-write-wins on the cart blob | Low latency, eventual | DynamoDB conditional writes, or Redis hash with versioning |
| Inventory decrement | Consistency, return an error if the shard is unreachable | Consistency | Serializable transaction or a single-writer partition, because overselling is unrecoverable |
| Social feed | Available, return slightly stale counts | Low latency | Fan-out on write, read from a materialized view |
| Payments ledger | Consistency | Consistency (latency budget is generous) | Postgres `SERIALIZABLE`/replicated SQL, append-only ledger with idempotency keys |
| Product page | Available, cached | Low latency | Cache-aside with a short TTL, so a stale read is bounded by design |

The mistake to name explicitly: applying one PACELC answer to a whole system. A real product is five to fifteen PACELC decisions, one per data class, and the senior skill is knowing which data classes cannot tolerate the "A" branch.

## 6.18.4 Consistency models, with concrete examples

| Model | Guarantee | Concrete example | How stores expose it |
|---|---|---|---|
| Strong / linearizable | After a write completes, every subsequent read anywhere sees it (or a later write); total order across all operations | Debit an account, then read the balance from any replica: you see the debit | Postgres with a single primary and synchronous/quorum-ish replicas; single-region DynamoDB `ConsistentRead: true` (a leader-free design uses a quorum); MongoDB `readConcern: "linearizable"` on the primary; etcd and ZooKeeper reads; Spanner/CockroachDB by default |
| Sequential | All nodes see writes in the same order, but reads may be stale | Replica B receives write 7 before read 5 is answered; order is preserved, freshness is not | MongoDB `readConcern: "majority"` (majority-committed, ordered), DynamoDB eventually consistent reads, Cassandra `ONE` reads |
| Causal | If A happens-before B, a reader that has seen A and then reads sees B | A user posts a comment, then edits it; a client that saw the post always sees the edit afterwards | MongoDB causal sessions (`startSession` + `causalConsistency: true`), Cassandra causal consistency via session timestamps and `CLUSTERING ORDER`/timestamps, DynamoDB transactional + ordered streams |
| Read-your-writes / monotonic reads | You never see your own data go backwards | After `UPDATE profile`, your profile read never returns the previous version | Replica-read routing with sticky sessions, MongoDB causal sessions, Redis single-node or a consistent-hash replica, or a write-then-read-your-own-node heuristic |
| Monotonic reads | Never see a value older than one you have already seen (but may skip forward) | Scrolling a feed never shows an older post again after a newer one | Session-pinned replica, or per-session "last seen version" tracking |
| Eventual | Converges if writes stop; no ordering, no bound in theory | A like count is 100 on the primary and 99 on a replica for 200ms | DynamoDB default reads, Cassandra `ONE`, S3-style eventual consistency, any async replication |

Two practical points that separate senior answers from textbook ones:

1. **"Eventual" is meaningless without a bound.** Say "eventual with a convergence SLO of under 2 seconds at p99, monitored by the replication-lag metric, with a read-your-writes override for the writer" rather than "eventually consistent".
2. **Consistency is paid per read.** Going from an eventually consistent to a strongly consistent read is a capacity and latency purchase (DynamoDB charges twice the RCUs; MongoDB's linearizable read requires a primary and a majority-committed read point). Design so that only the operations that need it pay for it.

## 6.18.5 Failure detectors

A distributed system cannot directly observe "the other node is down". It infers it from silence, which makes the detector a probability machine with two failure modes.

- **Failure detector components:** a heartbeat period (how often to probe or ack), a timeout (how long to wait before suspecting), and an *accusation* step (broadcast the suspicion so peers agree, otherwise different nodes suspect different nodes and split the cluster).
- **Phi accrual failure detector** (Cassandra, Akka): instead of a binary timeout, track an exponentially weighted distribution of heartbeat arrival intervals and report a *suspicion level* phi. phi > 8 is a common "declare failure" threshold. This adapts to jitter and GC pauses far better than a fixed timeout.
- **Why a fixed timeout is fragile:** a too-short timeout produces false positives, so healthy nodes are ejected and you get an availability blip for nothing; a too-long timeout means slow detection and a longer period of unavailable writes. Cross-reference 6.1–6.17 for how the same tradeoff shows up as `innodb_lock_wait_timeout`, `statement_timeout`, and Postgres `wal_sender_timeout`.
- **Timeouts must be hierarchical, not uniform.** RPC timeout < database query timeout < HTTP request timeout < client timeout, with real slack at each layer. Every incident traced to "the request took 30 seconds" usually began as a per-hop timeout that was too long, so failures piled up instead of shedding load.
- **Fencing:** a detector can be wrong. Any lock or lease must be protected by a monotonically increasing token (see 6.20) so a partitioned, later-recovered process cannot perform a destructive write after it lost the lease.
- **Acks are not receipts.** A client timing out does not mean the write failed; it may have succeeded and the ack was lost. Every mutating RPC needs an idempotency key, otherwise a retry double-charges a card.

## 6.18.6 Latency budgets

Say numbers in interviews, and derive them from a budget rather than from taste.

| Hop | p50 | p99 | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Same-datacentre RTT | 0.3ms | 1ms | Wire plus switch |
| Cross-AZ RTT | 1ms | 3ms | Also a cross-AZ bill |
| Cross-region RTT | 30ms | 80ms | Makes two-phase commits and chatty RPCs fatal |
| Primary Postgres indexed point read | 0.5ms | 3ms | Warm buffer pool |
| Postgres index scan returning 50 rows across a network | 3ms | 25ms | Row width and plan matter more than the scan |
| Redis `GET`, same node | 0.1ms | 0.5ms | Single-threaded: p99 is a queueing story, not a compute story |
| Cassandra `ONE` read, local DC | 2ms | 15ms | Coordination plus storage |
| MongoDB indexed find, primary, `w:1` | 1ms | 5ms | `w: majority` adds a replication round trip |
| DynamoDB strongly consistent GetItem | 3ms | 12ms | 2x the RCU of an eventual read |
| Elasticsearch search, 1M docs, 50 hits | 8ms | 60ms | Near-real-time segment state matters |
| Cross-region sequential RPCs (5 hops) | 150ms | 400ms | Budget killer; batch or collapse the chain |

**Method to say out loud:** "Start from the user's tolerance, say 200ms for a search-as-you-type keystroke. Reserve 150ms for everything, give the datastore 40ms at p99, cache hits must be under 5ms, and that leaves 10ms of headroom. It also means at most one remote hop, so the fan-out to a search cluster has to be either parallel or cached." Then: **the law of the dangling pointer** — a budget must be *decomposed*, and the sum must be enforced (deadlines propagated, cancellation on expiry, budget-aware retries with jitter, load shedding before timeouts).

## 6.18.7 Decision table: requirement to store

| Requirement | Start with | Because | Watch out for |
|---|---|---|---|
| Money movement, inventory decrement, anything where a duplicate or lost update is unacceptable | Postgres (6.1–6.17) with `SERIALIZABLE`/explicit locks and idempotency keys | You need transactions, constraints, and a single writer for correctness | Latency under contention; sharding a ledger is genuinely hard |
| One entity read by id, semi-structured fields, evolving shape, modest relational depth | MongoDB document store | One read per entity, schema agility, aggregation replaces most joins | N+1 from references, 16MB limit, unbounded arrays |
| Same as above but the team is small and data is relational | Postgres with `jsonb` + GIN | One engine, real transactions, real FKs, `jsonb` is MVCC'd and indexed | Write amplification on wide documents, weaker query ergonomics for deep paths, 1GB TOAST ceiling |
| Hot, tiny, read-mostly, or derived data (sessions, carts, counters, rate limits, locks, queues) | Redis | Microsecond single-node reads, atomic primitives, expiry for free | Single-node durability and memory ceiling, no joins, `maxmemory` eviction will eat your data if you mix classes |
| Massive write ingest, known query shapes, near-linear horizontal scale, multi-DC | Cassandra / wide-column | Write path is a local append per node, no coordinator hotspot on writes, tunable consistency per query | Denormalize aggressively, one table per query, compaction and tombstone load |
| Variable-depth relationship traversal as a core feature | Graph store (or a relational recursive CTE, or Redis `$graph`-style modules) | Depth is unbounded and the join cost is the problem the graph engine removes | Traversal blowups, no general query flexibility, operational novelty |
| Metrics, IoT, append-only events with time-window reads | TimescaleDB on Postgres, InfluxDB, or Prometheus TSDB | Time partitioning, retention policies, rollups are first class | Cardinality (metrics) and unbounded retention |
| Full-text relevance, faceting, typo tolerance, cross-field scoring | Elasticsearch/OpenSearch (or Postgres FTS first, see 6.25) | Inverted index, analyzers, relevance tuning, aggregations | Another source of truth, NRT lag, mapping explosion, reindex to change mappings |
| Semantic similarity over embeddings | pgvector first; hosted vector DB past a few million vectors and higher QPS | Keeps embeddings, rows, and permissions in one transactional engine with real joins | ANN recall, filter interaction, re-embedding cost |
| Global writes with region-local reads and no cross-region blocking | DynamoDB / Spanner / CockroachDB / Vitess-style | Engine handles partitioning, replication, and consensus | Cost, per-key model rigidity, no joins, hot partitions |

**Decision procedure to recite in an interview:**
1. List the top 5 access patterns with their read/write ratio, volume, latency target, and consistency requirement.
2. Identify the data classes with a hard correctness requirement (money, inventory, permissions) and put them in a transactional engine. Everything else is negotiable.
3. For the negotiable classes, ask: is the keyspace wide and the query set closed? If yes, a wide-column or key-value shape is cheap. If the shape is nested and you want ad-hoc field queries, a document store.
4. Ask what you would need to *delete* if you chose wrong, and what the migration path is (dual-write with an outbox, backfill, cutover, drop). "How would you undo this choice" is a senior question and almost nobody asks it.
5. Sanity-check the operationally boring truth: how many databases will this team run in 18 months, and who gets paged when one is down at 3am? A single Postgres with two or three well-chosen extensions is frequently the highest-scoring answer.

**Questions**
1. What is the NoSQL taxonomy, and what is each family's data unit and query model?
2. Why is "pick two of three" a misstatement of CAP?
3. What does PACELC add, and give a workload where you would accept staleness in a partition but not in the healthy case.
4. Explain linearizable vs sequential vs causal consistency, with a user-visible example of each.
5. "Eventually consistent" — what would you have to add to that sentence to make it engineerable?
6. How do stores expose consistency? Name the MongoDB, DynamoDB, and Cassandra knobs.
7. What is a failure detector, why can it not observe failure directly, and what does phi buy you?
8. Why must a lock be fenced, and how does a false failure detection turn into data loss without one?
9. Build a latency budget for a 200ms product page that fans out to Postgres, Redis, and Elasticsearch.
10. Requirement: seat booking with zero double-booking. Which store, and what consistency level per operation?
11. Requirement: social feed read by 50k users/sec. Which store, and what do you denormalize?
12. When is Postgres with `jsonb` and pgvector a better answer than five purpose-built services?
13. How do you decide, and what do you do when you realize you chose wrong?
14. What is the strongest argument that the correct answer is usually "one Postgres, several extensions"?

**Model answer, Q2:** "CAP is a theorem about behaviour *during* a network partition, not a menu. In any system spread over more than one network hop, partitions are a matter of time, so partition tolerance is mandatory and the real choice is what happens when nodes cannot talk. I prefer PACELC because it names the trade-off in the healthy case too, which is the state your system is in 99.9% of the time: if partitions exist you trade consistency for availability, and otherwise you trade consistency for latency. One more nuance I would add in a design review: 'C' is scoped per operation, not per database. A single product can have a linearizable payment write, a majority-committed profile read, an eventually consistent like counter, and a linearizable admin permission check, all in the same deploy. The skill is knowing which data class cannot accept the 'A' branch of the trade-off."

**Model answer, Q6:** "For MongoDB: `readConcern` of `local` or `available` (possibly stale), `majority` (majority-committed, and the default write concern pairs with it), `linearizable` (primary plus the most recent majority-committed data, at the cost of a round trip, and not usable in a transaction or against secondaries), and `snapshot` for a multi-document consistent read. Causal consistency is a session property. For DynamoDB, `ConsistentRead: true` on a table, doubling read capacity, which is unavailable on global secondary indexes and in some cross-region configurations. For Cassandra, ONE through ALL, plus the LOCAL_ variants, and the rule that the only thing protecting you from stale reads is the quorum math: R + W greater than the replication factor. I would expose consistency as a per-endpoint policy and log it, because 'eventually consistent' as a global label hides where the risk actually sits."

**Mistakes:** reciting CAP as a pick-two menu; claiming a system is "eventually consistent" without a convergence target; treating consistency as a database-wide switch; assuming a fixed heartbeat timeout is a failure detector; designing a lease without a fencing token; adding a new datastore without an invalidation or dual-write plan; saying "NoSQL scales horizontally" without naming the key; picking a graph database for a fixed two-hop join; ignoring that the answer must also be operable by the team.

**Mini task:** take a system you know (a music service, a food delivery app) and write a one-page data-class matrix: data class, store, consistency level, latency target, invalidation/derivation strategy, and what breaks first at 100x. Then measure real latencies for a Redis `GET`, a Postgres point read, and a `SELECT` of 50 rows locally, and check your table against the numbers; the gap between guess and measurement is the lesson.

---

# 6.19 Document Stores: MongoDB
Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition:** A store that persists self-describing documents, retrieves them whole, and indexes their internal fields. MongoDB is BSON-based and is the reference implementation people mean.

**The one-sentence mental model:** "MongoDB is a library of records where the record is an object, and a query returns a subset of that object's fields. Denormalize on purpose, because the cost of a document boundary is higher than most people assume."

## 6.19.1 BSON and `_id`

- **BSON** is a binary-encoded superset of JSON with explicit types: `int32`, `int64`, `double`, `decimal128`, `date`, `timestamp`, `ObjectId`, `binary`, `regex`, `minKey`/`maxKey`, `undefined`, `symbol`, `DBPointer`. `int64` vs `double` matters: `NumberLong` avoids the 2^53 precision cliff that JavaScript numbers create for large counters, and `NumberDecimal` avoids binary floating-point money arithmetic. Never store money as `double`; use integer minor units or `decimal128`.
- **Type coercion traps in queries:** `{ count: "5" }` does not match `{ count: 5 }` (except the numeric-type equivalence rules, where int, long, and double compare equal across types); `{ createdAt: "2026-01-01" }` does not match a `Date`; `true`/`1` do not match. Explicitly type your query values, because the driver's TypeScript types are not enforced at runtime by Mongo itself.
- **`ObjectId` is 12 bytes:** a 4-byte seconds timestamp, a 5-byte per-process random value, and a 3-byte counter. Consequences: ids created by different processes interleave arbitrarily, so an `_id` range scan is *approximately* time-ordered (fine for coarse time buckets, not fine-grained ordering), and there is no global monotonicity. If you need true ordering or exactly-once-ish inserts from concurrent writers, use an application-generated sortable id (ULID/UUIDv7) or a sequence, and know that UUIDv7 is time-ordered but leaks creation time.
- **`_id` is always indexed** and unique per collection. Uniqueness is per collection, not global, and a field named `_id` is immutable once set.
- **Key order matters for size and for compression** because BSON is serialized in document order, and the server compacts documents for storage. Putting the most-frequently-read small fields first is a real, measurable micro-optimization for cache-resident documents.

## 6.19.2 Embed vs reference: the actual decision rules

Embed when the child is **bounded**, **usually read with the parent**, and **not independently queried**.

| Signal | Embed | Reference |
|---|---|---|
| Cardinality | Bounded, "1:few" (few < 100) | Unbounded, or "1:many" where the many grows without limit |
| Read pattern | Child read only with parent | Child queried independently, filtered, sorted, or paginated |
| Write pattern | Child never updated on its own | Child updated at a different rate or by a different service |
| Consistency | Child must be atomic with parent | Child can lag; a join is acceptable |
| Growth | Array size is predictable and small | Array grows with traffic, time, or user behaviour |
| Size | Total stays well under 16MB | Total can approach the document limit |

**The unbounded-growth rule:** if a collection embeds anything that grows with time or with user activity — a post's comments, an order's line items, a user's sessions, a product's reviews, a log of events — the array will eventually hit the 16MB ceiling or destroy document-level cache locality. The three legitimate escapes are (a) a bucket document keyed by a range so the parent only holds bucket pointers, (b) a time-window split, or (c) a separate collection. A post with 500k comments is the canonical failure.

**Read and write amplification.** Embedding reads fast but makes every write to the child a rewrite of the entire parent document, in a single-document write, on one shard. If a 40KB order document holds 400 line items and you update one item's price, you pay a 40KB write plus index maintenance. Also, MongoDB uses a document-granularity storage engine, so *any* update to a doc is a new version until WiredTiger reaches an internal checkpoint; high-churn embedded arrays generate a lot of garbage. Reference the index cost model in 6.1–6.17: a wide row behaves like a row with a fat payload, and the same write-amplification reasoning from `HOT` updates and heap bloat applies.

**When embedding forces transactions:** when two documents must change together — an order header and its payment record, a user and their quota, two nodes of a double-entry ledger. Single-document atomicity is MongoDB's core guarantee, and the moment you need multi-document atomicity you are paying for a transaction: a replica set, a session, snapshot read concern, majority-ish write concern, and a real latency cost. If a large fraction of your writes need it, that is a strong signal the boundary is in the wrong place — either re-shape so the unit of change is one document, or use a relational store.

**Schema patterns worth knowing by name:**

| Pattern | Shape | Use when |
|---|---|---|
| Schema versioning | `{ schemaVersion: 2, ... }`, one collection, validators per version | The same document type drifts over years; readers branch on the version, writers only ever write the newest |
| Discriminated union | `{ kind: 'card', last4 } \| { kind: 'bank', iban }` in one field | Polymorphic payloads that are always read together; one index on `kind` for per-type queries |
| Bucketing | `posts` holds `bucket: <dayIndex>`, a bounded array of entries, plus `startAt`/`endAt` | Unbounded child collections: comments, events, messages. Trade per-document size for document count |
| Subset | Embed only the 5 newest comments; the rest are separate | Most reads want a preview, few need the full history. Add a `commentsCount` denormalized |
| Extended reference | Store the child's id *and* a few display fields (`authorName`, `priceAtPurchase`) | The read shows the child and the snapshot must reflect purchase time anyway (price, name at that moment) |
| Denormalized counter | `commentCount`, `followerCount` updated with `$inc` | Read-heavy counts on a hot path; accept drift, reconcile from an aggregate job. Never trust it for authorization or money |
| Materialized view collection | A background job or change stream writes a pre-joined feed doc | Fan-out reads too expensive to compute per request |

**Extended reference deserves a specific warning:** a snapshot field is correct-by-design for immutable facts (the price charged, the name shown on an invoice) and wrong-by-design for live data (an editable product name). Label every denormalized field with its freshness contract, or someone will trust it.

## 6.19.3 Indexes that matter, and the ESR rule

Indexes in MongoDB are ordinary B-trees per collection (plus specialized ones). Cross-reference 6.1–6.17 for B-tree behaviour; what changes here is the *number* of them you will write by hand.

| Index type | Example | Behaviour and caveats |
|---|---|---|
| Single field | `{ status: 1 }` | Fine; a low-cardinality field alone usually is not worth it |
| Compound | `{ tenantId: 1, createdAt: -1 }` | **ESR ordering: Equality fields, then Sort, then Range.** Equality-prefix indexes are reusable across queries that share the prefix |
| Multikey | `{ tags: 1 }`, `{ "items.sku": 1 }` | One array field per index. A compound index may contain **at most one** array field: two arrays would need a parallel array entry, and the server refuses with an error |
| Partial | `createIndex({ email: 1 }, { partialFilterExpression: { deletedAt: { $exists: false } } })` | Smaller index; the query must be a *superset* of the filter or the index is unusable. Great for soft deletes and rare statuses |
| Sparse | `{ middleName: 1 }, { sparse: true }` | Only indexes documents that have the field. Note: a `null` value counts as present, so sparse misses a real "missing" case that partial handles properly |
| TTL | `createIndex({ expiresAt: 1 }, { expireAfterSeconds: 0 })` | Background thread deletes expired docs roughly once a minute, so expiry is approximate; with `expireAfterSeconds: 0` the *field's own date* is the deadline, otherwise the field is a creation timestamp plus a fixed lifetime |
| Text | `createIndex({ title: "text", body: "text" }, { weights: { title: 10 } })` | One text index per collection, no sorting, no partial matches, stemming per language, and no sharding a text index by a non-prefix shard key cleanly. See 6.25 for when to move to Elasticsearch |
| Wildcard | `createIndex({ "$**": 1 })`, or `{ "$**": 1, "secret": 0 }` to exclude | Indexes unknown or newly added subfields. Useful for schemaless event payloads. Cannot be used for a covered query, and does not help queries that enumerate fields dynamically in some cases |
| Hidden | `createIndex({ legacy: 1 }, { hidden: true })` | Still maintained on write (so it still costs) but invisible to the query planner. The safe way to retire an index: hide, watch the metrics, then drop |
| Unique + partial | `createIndex({ email: 1 }, { unique: true, partialFilterExpression: { deletedAt: { $exists: false } } })` | Enforces uniqueness among live rows only, letting soft-deleted rows keep the old email |
| Collation | `createIndex({ name: 1 }, { collation: { locale: 'en', strength: 2 } })` | Case- and accent-insensitive matching, but the index is only used by queries with the *same* collation |

**The ordering rules, stated properly.** For a compound index used by a query with equality predicates, a sort, and a range:

1. Put equality fields first. They are the most selective prefix and make the index reusable for any subset of the equalities as a prefix.
2. Then the sort field, so the index provides the ordering and no in-memory `SORT` stage is needed.
3. Then range fields. Once a range predicate appears, later fields in the index are no longer usable for bounding the scan (only for filtering), so they contribute little.

The corollary people miss: **if the sort field and a range field compete, sort wins** (put the sort earlier), because an in-memory sort over many documents is an immediate 100MB-limit failure while a slightly wider range scan is merely slower. Also: a sort on a *different* field than the index, or a sort whose direction is the reverse of the index for one component, produces a `SORT` stage; MongoDB cannot walk an index backwards for a compound index when the leading component's direction is fixed.

**The index-cardinality pitfalls:**

- **Leading low-cardinality field is not automatically bad** — for a *compound* index it is excellent, because the equality prefix still makes it selective overall (`{ status: 1, createdAt: -1 }` over millions of docs beats `{ createdAt: -1 }` when you always filter on status).
- **Leading low-cardinality field in a single-field index is usually useless** for point lookups: `{ deleted: 1 }` matches half the collection. The index may still help a covered count or a sort.
- **Cardinality is not the whole story; size is.** A field with 10 distinct values but 1KB strings costs more index space and more cache misses than a field with 100k distinct small values.
- **Low-cardinality sort fields are a trap.** Sorting on `status` gives a poor plan; sorting on `createdAt` needs an index. If you genuinely need ordering by a low-cardinality field, include a tiebreaker in the compound index (`{ status: 1, createdAt: -1 }`) and sort by both.
- **Redundant indexes are pure cost.** `{ a: 1 }` is subsumed by `{ a: 1, b: 1 }` for lookups on `a`; but `{ a: 1, b: 1 }` cannot serve a sort on `b` alone, and `{ a: -1, b: 1 }` is not redundant with `{ a: 1, b: 1 }` because of the mixed direction. The `sortKeys` / index stats report is how you find these.
- **Each index is a second write path** per document, plus memory pressure in the WiredTiger cache, plus a thing to remember at 3am. Index count per collection is a design metric.

**Reading the explain output:**

```js
db.orders.find({ tenantId: 't1', status: 'paid' })
       .sort({ createdAt: -1 })
       .explain('executionStats');
```
Look for, in order:
1. `winningPlan` stage `IXSCAN` (good) vs `COLLSCAN` (bad; the collection is being scanned, often because an index is missing *or* the ESR order is wrong).
2. `IXSCAN` `keyPattern` matches your intended ESR order, and the sort is provided by the index (`SORT` stage absent, or `SORT_MERGE` if it merged per-index results).
3. `nReturned` vs `totalDocsExamined` and `keysExamined` vs `nReturned`. `keysExamined / nReturned` near 1 means the index is precise; a ratio of 1000 means it is scanning wildly. `totalDocsExamined > nReturned * 5` means the filter is not selective enough for this index.
4. A `SORT` stage with `sortPattern` means in-memory sorting: under 100MB it works, above it the query fails with a memory limit error unless you set `allowDiskUse` (see 6.19.5).
5. `totalDocsExamined == totalKeysExamined == 0` and a `COLLSCAN` with a small result means the collection is tiny and the planner is right; do not index a 12-document lookup table.

## 6.19.4 Index examples that matter in practice

```js
// tenant-scoped feed: equality on tenant + equality on kind, then range/sort on time
db.posts.createIndex({ tenantId: 1, kind: 1, createdAt: -1 });

// active sessions with a TTL; partial so the index is small
db.sessions.createIndex(
  { expiresAt: 1 },
  { expireAfterSeconds: 0, partialFilterExpression: { revokedAt: { $exists: false } } }
);

// unique among live documents only
db.customers.createIndex(
  { email: 1 },
  { unique: true, partialFilterExpression: { deletedAt: { $exists: false } } }
);

// text search with a title boost
db.articles.createIndex(
  { title: 'text', body: 'text' },
  { weights: { title: 5, body: 1 }, default_language: 'english' }
);

// coverage: avoid fetching documents for a projection
db.products.createIndex({ sku: 1, name: 1, price: 1 }, { unique: true });
// covered query: db.products.find({ sku: 'A1' }, { sku: 1, name: 1, _id: 0 }) -> PROJECTION_COVERED
```

## 6.19.5 Aggregation pipelines, real ones

The aggregation framework is MongoDB's answer to joins and reporting. The rules that matter: `$match` and `$project` as early as possible so the working set shrinks, `$sort` before `$group` when the index can help, and always set `allowDiskUse: true` plus a `maxTimeMS` for anything user-facing.

```js
// Top sellers per category for a tenant, in the last 30 days, no $lookup in the hot path
db.orders.aggregate([
  { $match: {
      tenantId: 't1',
      status: 'paid',
      createdAt: { $gte: ISODate('2026-09-01T00:00:00Z') },
  } },
  { $unwind: '$items' },
  { $group: {
      _id: { category: '$items.category', sku: '$items.sku' },
      qty:     { $sum: '$items.qty' },
      revenue: { $sum: { $multiply: ['$items.qty', '$items.unitPrice'] } },
      orders:  { $addToSet: '$_id' },
  } },
  { $project: { _id: 0, sku: '$_id.sku', category: '$_id.category',
               qty: 1, revenue: 1, orderCount: { $size: '$orders' } } },
  { $sort: { revenue: -1 } },
  { $limit: 100 }
], { allowDiskUse: true, maxTimeMS: 2000 });
```

```js
// $lookup with `let` + `pipeline`: index-driven and avoids the array-materialized form
db.customers.aggregate([
  { $match: { _id: 'c1' } },
  { $lookup: {
      from: 'orders',
      let: { cid: '$_id' },
      pipeline: [
        { $match: { $expr: { $eq: ['$customerId', '$$cid'] } } },
        { $sort: { createdAt: -1 } },
        { $limit: 10 },
      ],
      as: 'recentOrders',
  } },
  { $project: { name: 1, recentOrders: { createdAt: 1, total: 1, _id: 0 } } },
]);
```

```js
// $facet: one round trip, two independent result sets (page + facet counts)
db.products.aggregate([
  { $match: { tenantId: 't1', category: 'shoes', price: { $lte: 200 } } },
  { $facet: {
      items: [
        { $sort: { createdAt: -1 } },
        { $skip: 0 }, { $limit: 20 },
        { $project: { _id: 1, name: 1, price: 1 } },
      ],
      facet: [
        { $group: { _id: '$brand', count: { $sum: 1 } } },
        { $sort: { count: -1 } },
      ],
  } },
]);
```

```js
// $setWindowFields: running totals and ranks without a self-join
db.trades.aggregate([
  { $match: { symbol: 'ACME', ts: { $gte: ISODate('2026-09-01') } } },
  { $sort: { ts: 1 } },                       // required
  { $setWindowFields: {
      sortBy: { ts: 1 },
      output: {
        runningNotional: { $sum: { $multiply: ['$price', '$qty'] }, window: { documents: ['unbounded', 'current'] } },
        rank:            { $rank: { by: { qty: -1 } } },
        denseRank:       { $denseRank: { by: { qty: -1 } } },
        prevTs:          { $shift: { output: '$ts', by: -1, default: null } },   // 5.0+
      },
  } },
  { $match: { runningNotional: { $gt: 10_000_000 } } },
]);
```

```js
// $sample for "give me 20 random products" — the correct way, not a $natural shuffle
db.products.aggregate([{ $match: { tenantId: 't1' } }, { $sample: { size: 20 } }]);

// $graphLookup: one bounded reverse traversal (see 6.22 for when this stops being enough)
db.categories.aggregate([
  { $match: { _id: 'electronics' } },
  { $graphLookup: {
      from: 'categories',
      startWith: '$parentId',
      connectFromField: 'parentId',
      connectToField: '_id',
      as: 'ancestors',
      maxDepth: 5,
      depthField: 'depth',
  } },
]);
```

```js
// $merge: persist the aggregate as a new collection (a materialized view job's final stage).
// Not allowed in a transaction; whenMatched/whenNotMatched control conflict behaviour.
db.daily_sales.aggregate([...], { allowDiskUse: true });
db.orders.aggregate([
  ...,
  { $merge: {
      into: 'daily_sales',
      on: '_id',
      whenMatched: 'replace',
      whenNotMatched: 'insert',
  } },
]);
```

**The 100MB sort memory limit.** The aggregation framework and `find` sort both refuse to sort more than 100MB in memory (internal setting names: `internalQueryMaxBlockingSortMemoryUsageBytes` for blocking sorts, and a separate `internalQueryMaxPipelineMemoryUsageBytes` for pipeline stages such as large `$group`/`$sort` spills). When the limit is hit, the query errors with a memory limit message. Three options, in order of preference:

1. **Do not sort that much.** Add an index that provides the sort, add a `$match` that narrows the input, or use `$bucket`/`$group` to aggregate down before sorting.
2. `allowDiskUse: true` on the aggregate call, which lets blocking stages spill to temporary files. It works, it is much slower, and it writes to the temp directory, so watch disk.
3. Raise the internal memory limit — viable in a self-managed deployment, but you are trading RAM for spill, so it needs capacity planning.

`$group` has its own exposure: accumulators are in memory, and the group output is capped by the 16MB document limit, so `$group: { _id: null, all: { $push: '$x' } }` on a large collection is a memory bomb. Use `$out`/`$merge`, `$facet`, or `$group` with `$sum`/`$count` instead. `$lookup` before `$project` and `$facet` early to keep the working set small.

## 6.19.6 Multi-document transactions

```js
const session = client.startSession();
try {
  await session.withTransaction(async () => {
    const order = await orders.insertOne(
      { _id: oid, customerId, items, total, status: 'pending' },
      { session, writeConcern: { w: 'majority' } }
    );
    const inv = await inventory.updateOne(
      { _id: `${items[0].sku}`, available: { $gte: items[0].qty } },
      { $inc: { available: -items[0].qty } },
      { session }
    );
    if (inv.modifiedCount !== 1) throw new Error('OUT_OF_STOCK');   // aborts the whole txn
  }, {
    readConcern: { level: 'snapshot' },
    writeConcern: { w: 'majority' },
    readPreference: 'primary',
    maxCommitTimeMS: 5000,
  });
} finally { await session.endSession(); }
```

**What they cost and when they fail:**
- A transaction requires a **replica set** (a standalone server cannot run one) and pins all reads and writes to one session and one primary.
- Default read concern inside a transaction is `snapshot`; writes default to majority-ish behaviour. Both are deliberate, and both cost latency and throughput.
- The transaction lifetime is bounded by the **oplog window** (60 minutes in modern releases; anything older aborts) and by `transactionLifetimeLimitSeconds` (default 60s). Never hold one open across user input.
- **Do not iterate a cursor inside a transaction**; materialise the result with a bounded `toArray` or run the read outside. A large cursor plus a 60s ceiling is a guaranteed abort.
- Write conflicts abort the whole transaction; `withTransaction` retries only errors carrying the `TransientTransactionError` label. A `WriteConflict` (code 112) under contention is normal and retryable; a plain `DuplicateKey` is not, and retrying it will not help.
- **No DDL for collections or indexes inside a transaction** in the general case (creating collections and indexes is not allowed in a multi-document transaction; some `collMod`-style changes are). Schema changes go through a separate, coordinated step.
- Cross-shard transactions are supported (two-phase commit across participants) with much higher latency and a large failure surface.
- Do not nest transactions, and do not open two transactions on the same session.

**Interview framing:** "I use a transaction when two documents must agree and no cheaper boundary exists. Before I do, I ask whether I can re-shape the data so the unit of change is one document — usually the right answer is yes, e.g. pre-aggregating inventory per day per SKU, or writing a single 'order with lines' document. Transactions are the escape hatch, not the default architecture."

## 6.19.7 Change streams

```js
const stream = orders.watch(
  [{ $match: { operationType: { $in: ['insert', 'update', 'delete'] } } }],
  {
    fullDocument: 'updateLookup',                 // re-read the doc on update events
    fullDocumentBeforeChange: 'whenAvailable',    // 6.0+, needs pre/post images enabled
    maxAwaitTimeMS: 1000,                         // heartbeats so an idle stream is not closed
    resumeAfter: lastToken,                       // or startAfter for an invalidate event
  }
);

for await (const change of stream) {
  await handle(change);
  lastToken = change._id;                          // the resume token
}
```

- **Backed by the oplog**, so a change stream is a cursor over the replication log with a resume token. It survives primary elections and restarts, and it requires a replica set or a sharded cluster (never a standalone).
- **`resumeAfter` vs `startAfter`:** `resumeAfter` continues after a given event; `startAfter` starts after the last event in a stream that ended with an `invalidate` event (a collection drop or rename, which closes the stream).
- **`fullDocument: 'updateLookup'`** re-reads the document, costing a lookup and giving you the *current* state, not the state as of the event — so it is wrong for auditing. For exact audit history, enable `changeStreamPreAndPostImages` on the server and use `fullDocumentBeforeChange`.
- **`oplog` window is the retention bound.** A consumer that is down longer than the oplog window misses events permanently and must do a resync. Size the oplog for your worst realistic consumer outage, and monitor the consumer's resume-token age.
- **Limits:** a change stream dies if the collection is dropped or renamed (you get `invalidate` and must handle it), you cannot use it in a transaction, aggregations in the pipeline are limited, and `$lookup`-style heavy pipelines in the `watch()` filter push cost to the server per event. Keep the pipeline to `$match` on `operationType`, `ns.coll`, and cheap field predicates; do the heavy lifting in the consumer.
- **Not a message broker.** At-least-once delivery, no ordering across collections, no backpressure guarantees, no consumer groups. For anything that must not lose events, pair change streams with a durable sink, or use a real broker (6.32 messaging and event-driven architecture) and make the sink idempotent.

## 6.19.8 Read concerns, write concerns, read preference, causal sessions

**Read concern** (how fresh and how broad a read must be):

| Level | Behaviour | Cost / rule |
|---|---|---|
| `local` | Return data from the connected node; may be stale after a rollback | Cheapest. Read-your-writes on the primary only |
| `available` | Any available node, explicitly may be stale or rolled back | Only for analytics and caches |
| `majority` | Data acknowledged by a majority of the replica set; prevents reading data that could be rolled back | Extra wait; the sane default for anything that matters |
| `linearizable` | From the primary, the most recent majority-committed data | A majority-committed read point plus a primary round trip; doubles cost; **not** usable in a transaction or with a secondary read preference |
| `snapshot` | A consistent point-in-time view across documents, for multi-document reads | Read-only in practice, transaction-friendly, `atClusterTime` for cluster-wide reads |

**Write concern** (how durable an ack is):
- `w: 0` = fire and forget (no ack at all; a rolled-back write looks successful). `w: 1` = primary ack, which can still be rolled back on failover. `w: "majority"` = majority ack, which is what you want for anything you cannot reconstruct. `w: <number>` and tag-set `w` exist for custom quorums.
- `j: true` additionally waits for the journal, which matters for node-level crash durability, not for replica failures. `wtimeoutMS` bounds the wait and *raises an error* — set it, and treat the error as "outcome unknown", not "failed", which means retry with the same idempotency key.
- Every write should be retryable (`retryWrites` is on by default) and idempotent; that is what makes safe retries possible. Error codes worth recognizing: `NotWritablePrimary` / `PrimarySteppedDown` (retryable, the primary changed), `WriteConflict` (retryable), `DuplicateKey` (not retryable), `UnknownTransactionCommitResult` (commit, do not retry the whole thing).

**Read preference:** `primary` (default; fail-safe), `primaryPreferred`, `secondary`, `secondaryPreferred`, `nearest`. Add tag sets for topology-aware routing (`{ readPreferenceTags: [{ dc: 'eu-west' }] }`) and `secondaryPreferred` + `maxStalenessSeconds` for bounded lag. Sticky sessions (`readPreferenceTags` plus a session id) route a client to one replica so monotonic reads are preserved.

**Causal sessions:**
```js
const session = client.startSession({ causalConsistency: true });
await session.withTransaction(async () => { /* writes */ });
// a read in the same session (or a session with the same causally-consistent clusterTime
// via `afterClusterTime`) is guaranteed to see those writes
await orders.findOne({ _id }, { session });
```
The session carries a cluster-time timestamp; subsequent reads are issued at `afterClusterTime` so they cannot return a state older than what the session already saw. It gives read-your-own-writes and monotonic reads across members without paying for linearizability on every read. It does not make unrelated clients' reads fresh, and it does not survive long session lifetimes (the cluster-time horizon).

## 6.19.9 Sharding, replication, elections

**Replication and elections.** A replica set has one primary and secondaries. Writes go to the primary and are oplogged to secondaries. If the primary stops heartbeaking, an election is triggered (a new primary; a candidate must be caught up and win a majority) and the topology changes — the core of the primary-stepdown error class. Practical rules:
- `electionTimeoutMillis` (default 10000) must exceed the heartbeat period (default 2000) by a comfortable margin, and both must exceed your worst observed GC pause and network hiccup, or you get spurious elections.
- `priority: 0` marks a member as never-primary (read replicas). Pair that with `hidden: true` so reads are not routed to it.
- `tags` drive read-preference routing, so a `dc` tag per member lets you pin reads to a region.
- Plan maintenance with `rs.freeze()` (freeze elections for N seconds) before a node reboot, otherwise you cause an election and a write pause.
- With `w: "majority"`, acknowledged writes survive an election. With `w: 1`, an election can roll back a write the client was told succeeded. That is the single most important durability decision in a Mongo deployment.

**Shard key selection — the decision that is expensive to reverse:**
- The shard key must be **immutable, never missing, and always indexed**; a query without the shard key value hits every shard.
- Prefer a key with **high cardinality and even distribution** matching your dominant query. Range keys cluster; hashed keys spread.

| Key type | Distribution | Good for | Bad for |
|---|---|---|---|
| Range on a monotonic id or timestamp | Writes all land on the newest chunk, then it splits — write hotspots on one shard | Time-ordered scans, range queries | Uniform write load |
| Hashed on a high-cardinality field | Spreads writes evenly across the whole keyspace | High-ingest OLTP, uniform keys | Range queries (hashed keys destroy ordering), and no efficient sort on a non-shard key |
| Hashed with a tenant prefix (`{ tenantId: 1, bucket: 'hashed' }`) | Spreads *within* a tenant while keeping a tenant co-located for range scans | Multi-tenant where tenants differ hugely in size | Very large single tenants, which need sub-key sharding or logical partitioning |
| Zone sharding (one range per datacenter) | Deterministic locality | Data residency, per-region capacity | Load balancing across zones; you own the balancing problem |

- **Chunk balancing:** chunks (a shard key range's worth of data) are split when they exceed a size threshold, and the balancer moves chunks to equalize. Moves are online, and the balancer competes with production traffic, so you schedule it and can disable it per collection.
- **Targeted vs scatter-gather:** a query containing the full shard key is *targeted* — one shard, low latency, and the index on that shard is fully effective. A query missing the shard key is *scatter-gather*: `mongos` fans out to every shard, each returns up to `limit` rows (so `limit: 10` on a 20-shard cluster can pull 200 documents), each shard sorts in its own 100MB budget, and `mongos` performs a **blocking merge sort** to produce global ordering. Consequences: latency is the slowest shard, the sort memory limit applies, and a deep `skip` is brutal. `EXPLAIN` shows `SHARD_MERGE` and the `sortPattern` — if that sort is not your shard key, the query does not scale with cluster size.
- **Uniqueness and sharding:** a unique index must be prefixed by the shard key. A bare unique index on `_id` is only unique per shard, not globally, because `_id` is not a globally ordered key. Choose a globally unique key or include the shard key.
- **Rules that bite:** you cannot change a collection's shard key without re-sharding (reshardOne, or the newer resharding APIs); you cannot `drop` a sharded index that the shard key needs; scatter-gather `$group` and `$lookup` are single-shard-pipeline problems and are a common cause of a "works on one shard, times out on ten".

**Hard limits to know cold:**

| Limit | Value / shape | Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| Document size | 16MB | Bucket, chunk, or GridFS for bigger; a design constraint you meet in production, not in the interview |
| BSON sort memory | 100MB (configurable) | `allowDiskUse` or narrow the query |
| `$group` / `$out` result | Bounded by the 16MB document limit | Do not `$push` a large result into one document |
| Index key size | Bounded (a few KB per entry) | Long strings or many components in a compound key fail; index tokens, not blobs |
| Text index | One per collection; no sort; no partial match | Move real search to 6.25 |
| Multikey | At most one array field per index | Two arrays cannot be indexed together |
| Oplog window | Bounded by disk | Caps change-stream resume age and the transaction lifetime |
| Oplog entry | Bounded (16MB) | A single huge document can exceed it and break replication |
| Connection pool | Per-server pool, `maxPoolSize` default 100, `waitQueueTimeoutMS` default 30000 | Pool exhaustion shows up as 30-second hangs, not errors (see 6.29) |

**Postgres JSONB versus MongoDB — the honest comparison:**

| Dimension | MongoDB | Postgres with `jsonb` |
|---|---|---|
| Transactions | Multi-document, snapshot, but expensive; single-document atomicity is the cheap path | Everything is in one; cross-table and cross-JSONB updates are one transaction |
| Constraints | Application-level plus a document validator (schema validation), not FKs | Full FK, `CHECK`, `UNIQUE`, `EXCLUDE`, generated columns, and `jsonb` expression indexes |
| Query flexibility | Rich ad-hoc field predicates and aggregation | `jsonb` operators (`@>`, `?`, `jsonb_path_query`), GIN, plus real joins and SQL analytics |
| Write cost | Whole-document rewrite semantics; careful with embedded arrays | Row rewrite on update; `jsonb` values over ~2KB are TOASTed to a separate table, so large updates write more, but nothing pathological |
| Joins | Emulated (`$lookup` = nested loop, or denormalize) | Free and optimizable |
| Cross-document read consistency | Needs transactions or denormalization | Already guaranteed |
| Scaling story | Native sharding, but shard key is a one-way door | Partitioning, read replicas, Citus-style distribution, or a logical sharding layer; more work, more control |
| Tooling, tooling, tooling | Mature, but the maturity is uneven (transactions, change streams, and index behaviour have all been sharp edges) | The best-documented, most battle-tested engine in the industry; the whole ecosystem assumes it |
| Best fit | Deeply nested, entity-centric documents with fast, wide reads; or genuinely horizontal scale with a stable shard key | Almost everything else, especially when data is relational at the edges |

The pattern to give in interviews: **Postgres is the default; MongoDB is chosen when the read unit and the write unit are the same document and the document is genuinely nested and evolving.** The failure mode to confess knowledge of: teams pick Mongo for "flexibility", end up with a collection of 500MB-worth-of-relationships as embedded arrays, then discover they need transactions to keep it consistent and a migration to fix it.

**Questions**
1. What is BSON and why does it exist? Name three types JSON cannot express cleanly.
2. What is an `ObjectId` made of, and what ordering guarantees can you rely on?
3. Embed or reference: state the decision rules as a checklist.
4. What is the "unbounded growth" rule, and give three mitigations?
5. Explain read and write amplification from embedding, in terms of document storage.
6. When does embedding force you into a multi-document transaction?
7. What is a bucketed pattern and when would you use it over a separate collection?
8. What is an extended reference, and how do you decide which fields may go stale?
9. State the ESR rule and explain why sort goes before range.
10. What is a multikey index, and what is the parallel-array restriction?
11. Contrast partial, sparse, and TTL indexes; give a use for each.
12. What is a wildcard index, when is it right, and what does it not support?
13. How do you retire an index without dropping queries in production?
14. What is index cardinality and how does it change your compound index order?
15. Walk through a `db.find().explain('executionStats')` and say what each number should be.
16. Why do you put `$match` first in an aggregation pipeline, and where does `$project` go?
17. What does `$facet` buy you, and what does it cost?
18. What is the 100MB sort limit, and what are your three options when you hit it?
19. What does `$setWindowFields` replace, and what does it require?
20. When would you use `$merge` and why can it not be in a transaction?
21. What do transactions require, what are their limits, and what does `withTransaction` retry?
22. What is a resume token, and what happens if a consumer falls behind the oplog window?
23. `fullDocument: 'updateLookup'` sounds safe for audit. Why is it not?
24. Compare `majority`, `linearizable`, and `snapshot` read concerns on cost and use.
25. What does `w: 1` risk that `w: "majority"` does not?
26. What breaks if you do an interactive transaction and run `Promise.all` of writes on the global client? (see 6.24 for the ORM version)
27. What is a causal session, and what does it not guarantee?
28. What must a shard key satisfy, and what happens to a query that omits it?
29. Compare range and hashed shard keys, with a write-hotspot argument.
30. What is targeted vs scatter-gather, and what does `limit: 10` cost you on 20 shards?
31. Why must a unique index be prefixed by the shard key?
32. What is zone sharding, and when is it better than a hashed key?
33. How do you keep an election from rolling back acknowledged writes?
34. What is `rs.freeze()` for?
35. List eight MongoDB limits and their operational consequences.
36. When is Postgres + `jsonb` + GIN the better answer than MongoDB?
37. What is the single most common MongoDB modelling mistake, and its remedy?

**Model answer, Q3:** "My checklist is five questions. First, is the child bounded? If the array grows with time or user activity — comments, events, sessions, reviews — it is unbounded and must not be embedded, because it will hit the 16MB document limit and because a partial update rewrites the whole document. Second, is the child ever read without the parent? If I filter, sort, or paginate the child independently, it needs to be its own document with its own index, because I cannot page inside an array. Third, do the child and parent change together? If the child updates at a different rate or by a different service, embedding multiplies write amplification. Fourth, does atomicity matter? If the child must be consistent with the parent on every read, I either embed or I need a transaction, and a transaction is a design smell. Fifth, what is the total size and its growth? Under a few hundred small sub-documents, embedded is usually right. The pattern I fall back to for the unbounded case is bucketing: a parent holds pointers to bucket documents, each covering a time or id range, so the parent stays small and reads stay one or two round trips."

**Model answer, Q17:** "`$facet` runs multiple sub-pipelines over the same already-materialized input and returns a single document with one field per facet. The reason it exists is round trips: a search page needs a page of results *and* facet counts, and computing both with two queries means two consistent snapshots and twice the network and planner work. The pattern is a `$match` for the filters, then `$facet` with an `items` sub-pipeline doing sort/skip/limit/project and a `facet` sub-pipeline doing group/sort. The cost is that every downstream stage sees the full matched set, and each sub-pipeline's own sort memory counts against the same 100MB budget, so a facet over a large result set is one of the most common sources of memory-limit errors. I would also put the `$match` before the `$facet`, never inside it, and I would add `allowDiskUse: true` and `maxTimeMS` for anything user-facing."

**Mistakes:** saying "MongoDB is schemaless" (it is schema-*flexible with validators*; dynamic typing is a feature and a migration hazard); embedding unbounded arrays; leading a compound index with a sort field before the equality prefix; running `$lookup` before `$match`; expecting a write to be searchable instantly (that is a search-engine property, not Mongo's — for Mongo, `readConcern: linearizable`); assuming `w: 1` is durable; using `ALLOW FILTERING`-style full-partition reads (Cassandra, 6.21); shard keys chosen for write distribution while the dominant read omits the key; forgetting that a sharded collection's `_id` is not globally unique by default; treating change streams as a durable queue.

**Mini task:** model a `posts` + `comments` system in MongoDB three ways — naive embedded array, bucketed by day, and separate collection with a denormalized `commentCount`. For each, write the two real queries (feed page, post detail) with their pipelines, add the indexes, and measure with `explain('executionStats')` at 1M posts and 50M comments. Then insert a 60,000-element embedded array and watch the document approach the limit. Finally, implement a change-stream consumer with resume-token persistence and kill it for 90 seconds to see recovery behaviour.

---

# 6.20 Key-Value Stores: Redis and DynamoDB, plus caching and rate limiting
Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition:** Stores that answer "give me the value at this key" as fast as physically possible, plus the layer of caching, locking, and rate limiting built on top of them. This is the most immediately useful NoSQL knowledge for a full-stack engineer and the most likely to be drilled.

## 6.20.1 Redis data structures and what each one is for

| Type | Commands that matter | Use it for | Trap |
|---|---|---|---|
| String | `GET`, `SET`, `SETEX`, `SETNX`, `GETSET`, `MGET`, `MSET`, `INCR`, `DECRBY`, `INCRBYFLOAT`, `APPEND` | Values, counters, JSON blobs, idempotency keys, locks, rate limits | Storing big JSON blobs and rewriting them on every read-modify-write; no partial update |
| List / deque | `LPUSH`, `RPUSH`, `LPOP`, `RPOP`, `LRANGE`, `LTRIM`, `LMOVE`, `BLPOP` | Bounded queues, recent-N activity, simple work queues | `LRANGE 0 -1` on a 2M-element list blocks the server; always `LTRIM` or use paging |
| Set | `SADD`, `SREM`, `SISMEMBER`, `SMEMBERS`, `SINTER`, `SUNION`, `SDIFF`, `SRANDMEMBER` | Tags, permissions, unique visitors, "who liked this", set algebra (intersections for feed candidates) | `SMEMBERS`/`SUNION` on a huge set is O(N) and blocking |
| Hash | `HSET`, `HGET`, `HMGET`, `HDEL`, `HINCRBY`, `HSCAN`, `HGETALL` | Per-field updates on an object, counters per field, sparse attributes | `HGETALL` on a 100k-field hash |
| Sorted set (zset) | `ZADD`, `ZRANGE ... WITHSCORES`, `ZREVRANGE`, `ZRANGEBYSCORE`, `ZRANK`, `ZREMRANGEBYSCORE`, `ZINCRBY`, `ZRANGEBYLEX` | Leaderboards, rate-limit windows, priority queues, "top N by score", sliding window logs, delay queues | Score-based uniqueness is not enforced (two members can share a score) |
| Bitmap | `SETBIT`, `GETBIT`, `BITCOUNT`, `BITOP`, `BITPOS` | Daily sign-in flags, feature-flag cohorts, boolean-per-user matrices; 1 bit per user so 1B users = 125MB | Offset arithmetic errors, and `BITCOUNT` on a huge key is O(N) |
| HyperLogLog | `PFADD`, `PFCOUNT`, `PFMERGE` | Approximate unique counts (2^13 = 8192 registers, ~0.81% error, 12KB per HLL) | Not exact: never use for billing or unique-constraint enforcement; `PFCOUNT` over many HLLs has a compounding error |
| GEO | `GEOADD`, `GEORADIUS`, `GEOSEARCH`, `GEODIST` | Nearby search on a sphere | Uses a geohash grid, not a true index; radius queries scan buckets. PostGIS is a better answer at scale |
| Stream | `XADD`, `XREAD`, `XREADGROUP`, `XACK`, `XPENDING`, `XAUTOCLAIM`, `XLEN`, `XTRIM` | Durable-ish consumer groups, at-least-once delivery, pending-entry recovery | Oplog growth if you never `XACK`/`XTRIM`; at-least-once, so consumers must be idempotent |
| Pub/Sub | `SUBSCRIBE`, `PUBLISH` | Fan-out of ephemeral events (cache invalidation hints, live notifications) | Fire-and-forget: no persistence, no delivery guarantee, no backlog. If a subscriber is down it misses the message |

**The mental model for choosing:** a string if you never need to touch part of it; a hash if you update fields independently; a set if membership is the query; a zset if you need ordering by a score or time; a stream if you need a consumer group with acknowledgement.

## 6.20.2 Single-threaded execution, pipelining, and blocking

- Redis executes commands on a **single thread** (with I/O threads in recent versions for reading and writing, and background threads for persistence and replication), so a single instance is *serialized by design*. That is why a Redis `GET` has a sub-millisecond p50 with no lock contention: there is no shared-nothing parallelism to coordinate.
- The consequence is the failure mode: **one slow command stalls every other client**. A `KEYS *` on a 10M-key database, a huge `HGETALL`, a `SORT` without `LIMIT`, a `SMEMBERS` on a giant set, or Lua that loops a million times will block the event loop for hundreds of milliseconds. Use `SCAN` with `COUNT` instead of `KEYS`, `SSCAN`/`HSCAN`/`ZSCAN` instead of the blocking variants, and always bound `SORT` with `LIMIT` (or use `ZRANGE`).
- Memory is the other single resource. Redis is in-memory: 100GB of data is 100GB of RAM plus overhead, and the allocator fragmentation plus copy-on-write during fork can double the footprint. This is why persistence (RDB snapshot) on a large dataset has a latency spike and why `maxmemory` policy decisions are an SRE conversation.
- **Pipelining** sends many commands in one round trip, without waiting for individual replies. With network RTT at 0.3ms, 1000 sequential `GET`s cost 300ms; pipelined they cost about one RTT plus server time.
  ```ts
  const p = client.pipeline();
  for (const id of ids) p.json.get(`user:${id}`);
  const results = await p.exec();   // [ [err, val], ... ]
  ```
  Rules: 10 to 100 commands per pipeline is the sweet spot (larger batches increase the head-of-line cost of one slow reply), do not pipeline unbounded, and check each reply for errors because `exec` does not throw.
- **`MULTI/EXEC` is a transaction only in the narrowest sense:** it guarantees the queued commands run in order without interleaving with other clients' commands. It does *not* roll back on error, it does not give isolation from reads outside the queue, and a `WATCH`ed key changed by another client aborts the whole thing. Modern code usually prefers a single Lua script or a function, because one round trip and atomic execution beats queue-then-exec.
- Real atomicity primitive: **Lua scripts and functions**, which execute atomically on the main thread. Keep them short; a long script is a long stall.

## 6.20.3 Persistence, replication, Sentinel, Cluster

| Mechanism | What it is | Trade-off |
|---|---|---|
| RDB | Point-in-time binary snapshot, written by a background fork, `BGSAVE` | Compact and fast to load; **loses everything since the last snapshot** |
| AOF `everysec` | Append every write to a log, fsync about once a second | At most ~1s of data loss; larger file, slower recovery; the usual production default |
| AOF `always` | fsync per write | At most one command lost; throughput cost, and a group-commit trick is required for sanity |
