| No persistence | Pure cache | Fine for a rebuildable cache; wrong for anything you cannot recompute |
| Replication | Async primary-to-replica stream | Reads scale, failover possible; **replication lag means a failover can lose acknowledged writes** |
| Sentinel | Monitoring, notification, and automatic failover for a primary/replica set | The standard HA setup; a failover still drops in-flight writes unless you use `min-replicas-to-write` and `WAIT` |
| `WAIT n timeout` | Block until n replicas acked the writes | Turns async replication into a tunable quorum at the cost of latency; the honest way to avoid data loss on failover |
| Cluster | Hash-slot partitioning (16384 slots), multi-key ops, resharding | Scales writes; adds `MOVED`/`ASK` handling, cluster-state gossip, no multi-key transactions across slots, and resharding is a live operation |
| Cluster-aware client (e.g. `ioredis` cluster mode) | Routes by key slot, follows `MOVED`, uses `ASK` during resharding | You must use one; a naive client against a cluster cluster silently fails |

- **Cluster errors you must know:** `MOVED <slot> <host:port>` (the slot lives elsewhere permanently; a good client follows it and caches the mapping), `ASK <slot> <host:port>` (the slot is migrating; issue the command only against that node for this one request, do not cache), `CROSSSLOT` (a multi-key command or Lua script touched keys in different slots — fix it with **hash tags**: `{user:42}:profile` and `{user:42}:prefs` hash to the same slot), `CLUSTERDOWN` (not enough replicas covering enough slots to serve the requested consistency), `TRYAGAIN`/`CLUSTERDOWN` during resharding.
- **`maxmemory` and eviction.** When memory hits `maxmemory`, Redis evicts per the configured `maxmemory-policy`, and this is a data-loss event for anything that is not rebuildable.

| Policy | Evicts | Best for | Worst for |
|---|---|---|---|
| `noeviction` | Nothing; writes error out | Instances holding data of record, or mixed workloads where you want loud failures | Caches at capacity — you get errors instead of serving |
| `allkeys-lru` | Any key, least recently used | A pure cache instance | Anything you cannot lose |
| `allkeys-lfu` | Any key, least frequently used | Skewed access (a few keys dominate) | Recent-but-rare keys, e.g. new deploys |
| `allkeys-random` | Random | Nothing in particular | Predictable behaviour |
| `volatile-lru` / `volatile-lfu` / `volatile-random` | Only keys with a TTL set | Mixed instances where only some keys are disposable | Non-expiring keys pin memory and can make the instance unable to evict anything, so writes start erroring |
| `volatile-ttl` | Keys with the nearest TTL | Time-bounded data | Same |

**Two rules that save systems.** (1) **Separate instances by data class**: a cache instance (`allkeys-lru`, no persistence) separate from a rate-limiter instance (`noeviction` or a volatile-only policy with a large cap) separate from a session store. Otherwise the cache's eviction storm eats your sessions, or the rate limiter's growth evicts the cache and takes the site down. (2) **Do not store large values.** A 1MB value in a 1GB instance is 1% of your memory and it will be replicated, copied on rewrite, and evicted unpredictably. Keep values small and put the bulk in object storage.

## 6.20.4 DynamoDB: keys, access-pattern-first design

- **Two keys, both mandatory:** a partition key (hash) and an optional sort key (range). The partition key must be an equality match — `query` requires it; a missing partition key means `scan`. The sort key supports `=`, `begins_with`, `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`, `between`, and the `sortOrder` variant of `begins_with` for pagination.
- **Design the table from the access patterns, not the entities.** Write the queries first, then decide the key schema. This is the "access-pattern-first" discipline and the thing that makes a single-table design coherent.

```
PK                    SK                        Entity
────────────────────  ────────────────────────  ─────────────────────────
TENANT#t1             PRODUCT#p900              Product record
TENANT#t1             ORDER#2026-000123          Order header
TENANT#t1             ORDER#2026-000123#ITEM#1   Order line
TENANT#t1             USER#u77                   User profile
TENANT#t1             USER#u77#ORDER#2026-000123 User-order link (feed)
```
Two queries, one table:
```
Query PK = "TENANT#t1" AND begins_with(SK, "ORDER#2026-000123")
     -> the header and every line item, in one strongly-consistent read
Query PK = "TENANT#t1#USER#u77" AND begins_with(SK, "ORDER#")
        -> one user's orders, newest first with ScanIndexForward = false
```

| Operation | What it does | Cost / limit |
|---|---|---|
| `GetItem` | Read by full key | Half the RCU of an eventual read; linearizable if requested |
| `Query` | Read a partition, ordered | No cross-partition queries; `Limit` applies per call, not per page of results |
| `Scan` | Read everything, filtered **after** the read | `FilterExpression` does not save RCUs; never in a request path; use it for backfills and `SELECT COUNT(*)`-style jobs |
| `BatchGetItem` | Up to 100 items in one call | 16MB response limit; retry unprocessed keys with backoff |
| `BatchWriteItem` | Up to 25 items | Not atomic; duplicate-key errors on unprocessed items; no ordering guarantees |
| `TransactWriteItems` | Up to 25 unique items, 4MB each, 100 total items | 2x the WCU cost; **cannot** touch a GSI or LSI; no `Scan`-based condition |
| `UpdateItem` | Partial update, `ADD`/`SET`/`REMOVE`/`DELETE` | `ADD #c :one` is an atomic counter; conditions make it a compare-and-swap |
| `ExecuteStatement` / PartiQL | SQL-ish statements | Convenient for batch and transaction patterns; still key-schema-bound |

- **Hot partitions.** A single partition is hard-capped at **1,000 WCU/s and 1,000 RCU/s** (with a 3,000 WCU/s write burst allowance in provisioned mode). One hot key means one third of your throughput regardless of table size. Diagnose with CloudWatch's `SuccessfulRequestLatency` and `ThrottledRequests` per partition, or by looking for one item dominating.
  Fixes, in order of preference: (1) **change the key** so the hot entity is no longer alone in its partition — put a time bucket in the sort key (`ORDER#{yyyymmdd}#{seq}`) so yesterday's and today's orders are separate rows; (2) **write-shard** with a small random/sequence prefix and fan out reads across N logical shards, fanning back in and picking the best answer; (3) move the high-QPS read path into DAX (see below); (4) for reads, replicate the source item into multiple partitions and query them in parallel; (5) finally, split the table.
- **Projections and secondary indexes.** A base table carries the full item. An **LSI** shares the base table's partition key and sort key capacity, costs no extra write capacity for maintenance, and only supports eventually consistent reads, but doubles the write I/O on disk. A **GSI** is a separate table with its own partition/sort key, consumes its own read and write capacity, supports only eventually consistent reads, and can be `KEYS_ONLY`, `INCLUDE` (a subset of non-key attributes), or `ALL`. GSIs are how you get more access patterns; they are also where the "eventually consistent read right after a write" surprise comes from.
  - **Sparse indexes** (only items that have all of the index key attributes) are the trick for a GSI where only a subset of items belongs to it, e.g. an index over `gsi1pk`/`gsi1sk` set only on orders. Then `GSI1` genuinely contains only orders, and the GSI stays small.
- **Consistency.** `ConsistentRead: true` costs 2x the RCU, is invalid on GSIs and LSIs, and is invalid on Global Tables secondaries. The failure mode to remember: a read immediately after a write, with `ConsistentRead` omitted, can return **nothing at all** for that key. Retrying fixes it; "the write is not there" is the symptom, not "the write failed".
- **Conditional writes** are the correctness tool:
  ```
  ConditionExpression: "attribute_not_exists(PK) OR #v = :expected"
  ```
  giving you optimistic concurrency: increment a counter only if the value is what you read, transfer only if the balance is sufficient, and make registration idempotent. `attribute_exists(PK)` for "create if absent". `ReturnValuesOnConditionCheckFailure: "ALL_OLD"` returns the current item so you can tell the caller how far off they were.
- **Capacity modes.** Provisioned with auto scaling tracks utilization per dimension (WCU and RCU scale independently, target utilization configurable, 20% minimum floor) and is cheaper at steady state but can be throttled when growth outruns the scaling reaction. On-demand prices roughly 2x per unit but is right for spiky, unpredictable, or development traffic; the catch is a double-rate charge on sudden spikes above twice the trailing peak. Reserved capacity is for a known baseline.
- **TTL** deletes an item after `expiresAt` on a number (epoch seconds); it is best effort, asynchronous, and may take up to 48 hours, so never treat TTL as a correctness mechanism — treat it as a garbage collector and make reads tolerate an item that has not yet vanished.
- **Streams** give an ordered, at-least-once log of every write (24-hour retention) per shard, which is the basis for change-data capture into a warehouse, a search indexer, or a cache invalidator. `StreamViewType` of `NEW_IMAGE`, `OLD_IMAGE`, `NEW_AND_OLD_IMAGES`, or `KEYS_ONLY` lets you pay only for what you need. The classic design problem: the DB and the stream are two copies of the truth, so every consumer must be idempotent and must handle the case where the stream lags behind the table (read from the table first if you need the latest state, then apply the event).
- **DAX** is an in-memory micro-cache in front of DynamoDB: per-item micro-TTL (seconds), request collapsing, and a cluster of cache nodes up to 1TB each. It gives effectively consistent reads for hot items within the micro-TTL, cuts RCU cost dramatically for read-heavy skewed workloads, and needs `DescribeTable` permission or schema files for auto-discovery. It is read-only caching: it does not make writes faster, and it explicitly does not serve strongly consistent reads, so the "I read my own write through DAX" case still needs care.

## 6.20.5 Caching patterns

| Pattern | Read path | Write path | Failure mode | Use when |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Cache-aside** | Look in cache; on miss, load from the store, populate, return | Invalidate or update after the write | Miss storm on expiry; stale reads until invalidation | The default. Keeps the store authoritative and the cache disposable |
| **Read-through** | Cache loads on miss itself (the cache calls the loader) | Invalidate | Same as cache-aside, but the logic lives in the cache layer | You want one code path for all cached reads, e.g. a shared library |
| **Write-through** | n/a | Write to the store *and* the cache synchronously | Cache write failure fails or complicates the request; dual-write divergence | You cannot tolerate a stale read after a successful write, and the write path is not hot |
| **Write-back (write-behind)** | n/a | Write to the cache, flush to the store asynchronously or in batches | Data loss if the process dies before flush; ordering and idempotency problems | Counters, view counts, metrics, anything aggregatable where a small loss window is fine |
| **Write-around** | n/a | Write only to the store; do not fill the cache | Next read is always a miss | Data unlikely to be re-read, or you want to avoid polluting the cache with one-shot reads |
| **Read-through + SWR** | Serve stale immediately, refresh in the background | Invalidate | A refresh can be lost or slow; stampede on stale expiry | Pages where a few seconds of staleness beats a slow first paint |

**Cache-aside, the code that is actually right** (this is the canonical 6.27 exercise; see it there for the from-scratch version):

```ts
async function getProduct(id: string): Promise<Product | null> {
  const key = `product:${id}`;
  const hit = await redis.get(key);
  if (hit !== null) return JSON.parse(hit) as Product;
  const product = await db.product.findUnique({ where: { id } });
  if (product) await redis.set(key, JSON.stringify(product), { EX: 300, NX: false });
  return product;
}
```
The details that matter: a **negative cache** for misses (a short-TTL `NULL` sentinel) so a flood of lookups for a nonexistent id cannot hammer the store; **TTL jitter** (`300 + random(60)`) so thousands of keys loaded together do not all expire on the same tick; and **bounded value size** so one huge object cannot evict everything else.

- **TTL plus jitter** is not optional at scale. Fixed TTLs loaded by a batch expire simultaneously and produce a periodic stampede; adding ±20% random jitter spreads expiry and reduces peak miss rate by a large factor.
- **Stampede protection**, three techniques:
  1. **Single-flight** (in-process): a map of in-flight promises per key; concurrent misses share one load. Cheapest and it fixes the single-process case.
  2. **Distributed lock**: `SET lock:key <token> NX PX 5000`; the winner loads, others sleep briefly and retry the cache. Fixes the multi-process case. Needs a token-checked release (Lua, below) and a bounded wait, and the TTL must exceed the worst expected load time.
  3. **Early recompute**: when an entry is within 20% of expiry, the *first* reader triggers the refresh while serving the current value. Turns a synchronized miss into a smooth trickle.
  Combine single-flight with early recompute and you rarely need a lock.
- **Invalidation strategies:**

| Strategy | Mechanism | Cost | Failure mode |
|---|---|---|---|
| TTL only | Entries expire | Simplest, self-healing | Staleness up to the TTL; a wave of misses at expiry |
| Event-driven delete | Publisher emits an event on write; subscribers `DEL` the keys | Fast, explicit | Lost events (use a durable broker or the DB outbox), N subscribers, event ordering |
| Write-through update | Writer updates the cache in the same operation | No staleness window | Dual-write failure modes, hot write key, contention |
| Versioned keys | Key includes a version: `product:7:{v42}`; the writer bumps `product:7:v` and the reader passes the version | Naturally race-free: an old writer cannot clobber new data | Key space grows (bounded by TTL), version lookup needed, garbage |
| Generation/token | Store a monotonic generation; readers embed it and a mismatch forces a re-read | Cheap, no extra writes | Needs a reliable generation source |
| Pub/Sub hint | `PUBLISH cache:invalidate product:7` and hope | Very cheap | Fire-and-forget; misses are possible. Good as an optimization on top of TTL, never as the only mechanism |
- **Distributed locks in Redis:**
  ```ts
  // acquire
  const token = crypto.randomUUID();
  const ok = await redis.set(`lock:order:${orderId}`, token, { NX: true, PX: 10_000 });
  if (ok !== 'OK') throw new ConflictError('resource busy');

  // release: only if we still own it (Lua is one atomic round trip)
  const RELEASE = `
    if redis.call('GET', KEYS[1]) == ARGV[1] then
      return redis.call('DEL', KEYS[1])
    else
      return 0
    end`;
  await redis.eval(RELEASE, 1, `lock:order:${orderId}`, token);

  // extend: only if we still own it
  const EXTEND = `
    if redis.call('GET', KEYS[1]) == ARGV[1] then
      return redis.call('PEXPIRE', KEYS[1], ARGV[2])
    else
      return 0
    end`;
  await redis.eval(EXTEND, 1, `lock:order:${orderId}`, token, '10000');
  ```
  Three rules that are non-negotiable: **the TTL** (a crash must not deadlock the key forever), **the unique token** (so process A cannot release process B's lock), and **release in a `finally`**.
  **Redlock caveats:** Redlock (N independent masters, a majority, per-node locks) is argued to be unsafe for mutual exclusion when a process can pause (a long GC stop, a descheduled VM) between acquiring the lock and acting on it — a paused client can come back after its lock has expired and stolen, and perform the write anyway. This is why fencing tokens exist, and why the Redis project's own guidance distinguishes "efficiency locks" (use a single instance plus `SET NX PX` with a token) from "correctness locks" (use a real consensus system, and always fence).
  **Fencing tokens:** a monotonically increasing number, checked by the resource being protected.
  ```ts
  const fence = await redis.incr('fence:invoice:42');   // 1, 2, 3, ...
  await redis.set(`lock:invoice:42`, `${holder}:${fence}`, { NX: true, PX: 5000 });
  // the storage layer rejects any write carrying a fence lower than the last one it saw
  await db.invoice.updateMany({ where: { id: 42, lastFence: { lt: fence } }, data: { status, lastFence: fence } });
  ```
  **The alternative that is often better:** if you already have Postgres, use **advisory locks** — a database-level mutual-exclusion primitive, released automatically when the connection closes, so a crashed process cannot leak a lock.
  ```sql
  SELECT pg_try_advisory_lock(hashtext('invoice:42'));   -- session-level, must unlock explicitly
  SELECT pg_advisory_xact_lock(hashtext('invoice:42'));  -- auto-released at COMMIT/ROLLBACK
  -- two-key form avoids hash collisions: pg_try_advisory_lock(4, 42)
  ```
  Gotcha: a session-level advisory lock on a *pooled* connection is a bug waiting to happen — if the transaction that acquired it is rolled back to the pool with the lock still held, another process is now blocked forever. Use the `xact` variant inside a transaction, or guarantee `pg_advisory_unlock` in a `finally` on the same checked-out connection.

## 6.20.6 Rate limiting: five algorithms and when each is right

Rate limiting is the canonical systems-design drill and the most concrete Redis work in this phase. State the algorithm, its memory cost, its precision, and its failure mode.

| Algorithm | Memory per key | Precision | Burst behaviour | Cost | Use when |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Fixed window counter | 1 counter | Approximate; allows 2x the limit across a boundary | Bad: a client can send `limit` at the end of one window and `limit` at the start of the next | Cheapest, 2 commands | Rough protection, internal APIs, coarse quotas |
| Sliding window log | 1 zset entry per request in the window | Exact | Perfectly smooth | Highest memory, O(log N) per request | Small limits, expensive endpoints, abuse detection |
| Sliding window counter | 2 counters | Approximate; weighted interpolation | Much better than fixed; ≤ the limit within roughly one request per `w/L` | Nearly as cheap as fixed | **The general-purpose default** |
| Leaky bucket | Queue of items or a water level | Perfectly smooth output rate | No burst at all | A queue to drain | Smoothing outbound load to a fragile downstream |
| Token bucket | 1 counter (level + timestamp) | Exact in the limit, allows a defined burst | Bursts up to the bucket capacity | Cheap, 1 Lua call | APIs, per-user quotas, anything with a burst allowance |

**Fixed window** — the bug everyone should be able to name without thinking:
```ts
// rate: 100/minute
const key = `rl:fixed:${userId}:${Math.floor(Date.now() / 60_000)}`;
const n = await redis.incr(key);
if (n === 1) await redis.expire(key, 120);       // 2x window covers the boundary
if (n > 100) throw new TooManyRequests();
```
With a 1-minute window and a limit of 100, a client can send 100 at 10:00:59.999 and 100 more at 10:01:00.001: 200 requests in 2ms. The fix is not a longer TTL; it is a sliding algorithm.

**Sliding window log** — exact, expensive, and the right answer when the limit is small (say 10 requests per minute for an expensive endpoint):
```ts
const LUA_LOG = `
local key      = KEYS[1]
local now      = tonumber(ARGV[1])
local window   = tonumber(ARGV[2])
local limit    = tonumber(ARGV[3])
local member   = ARGV[4]
redis.call('ZREMRANGEBYSCORE', key, '-inf', now - window)
local used = redis.call('ZCARD', key)
if used >= limit then
  local oldest = redis.call('ZRANGE', key, 0, 0, 'WITHSCORES')
  local retry = 0
  if oldest[2] then retry = math.ceil(tonumber(oldest[2]) + window - now) end
  redis.call('PEXPIRE', key, window)
  return { 0, used, retry }
end
redis.call('ZADD', key, now, member)
redis.call('PEXPIRE', key, window)
return { 1, used + 1, 0 }
`;

async function slidingLog(userId: string, limit = 10, windowMs = 60_000) {
  const now = Date.now();
  const member = `${now}-${crypto.randomUUID()}`;   // unique: same-ms writes must not overwrite
  const [allowed, used, retryMs] = await redis.eval(
    LUA_LOG, 1, `rl:log:${userId}`, now, windowMs, limit, member
  ) as [number, number, number];
  return { allowed: allowed === 1, used, retryAfterMs: retryMs };
}
```
Memory: `limit` entries per active key. At 1M active users with a 10-request limit that is 10M zset entries — which is why this is not the default for a high-traffic public API.

**Sliding window counter** — the default choice: the accuracy of a log at the cost of two counters. The estimate is `current + previous * (1 - fractionElapsed)`.
```ts
const LUA_SWC = `
local now    = tonumber(ARGV[1])
local w      = tonumber(ARGV[2])
local limit  = tonumber(ARGV[3])
local start  = now - (now % w)

local function read(k)
  local r = redis.call('HMGET', k, 's', 'c')
  local s, c = tonumber(r[1]), tonumber(r[2])
  if s == nil then return nil, 0 end
  return s, c
end

local cs, cc = read(KEYS[1])
local ps, pc = read(KEYS[2])

if cs ~= start then
  if cs == start - w then
    pc = cc                 -- the old current window becomes the previous one
  else
    pc = 0                  -- a gap: nothing in the trailing window
  end
  cc = 0
  redis.call('DEL', KEYS[2])
  redis.call('HSET', KEYS[1], 's', start, 'c', 0)
end

local elapsed = (now - start) / w
local est = cc + pc * (1 - elapsed)

if est >= limit then
  redis.call('PEXPIRE', KEYS[1], w * 2)
  redis.call('PEXPIRE', KEYS[2], w * 2)
  return { 0, tostring(est) }
end

cc = cc + 1
redis.call('HSET', KEYS[1], 's', start, 'c', cc)
redis.call('PEXPIRE', KEYS[1], w * 2)
redis.call('PEXPIRE', KEYS[2], w * 2)
return { 1, tostring(est + 1) }
`;

async function slidingCounter(userId: string, limit = 100, windowMs = 60_000) {
  // Hash tags keep both keys in one slot so the script is Cluster-safe.
  const base = `rl:swc:{${userId}}`;
  const res = await redis.eval(LUA_SWC, 2, `${base}:c`, `${base}:p`,
                              Date.now(), windowMs, limit) as [number, string];
  return { allowed: res[0] === 1, estimate: Number(res[1]), limit };
}
```
Accuracy: worst case it over-estimates by at most the previous window's count, decaying linearly; the classic illustration is a client at 60 requests/minute with a limit of 100 across two windows — the estimator can see up to ~110. Good enough for a public API, wrong for a billing quota.

**Token bucket** — the right answer when bursts are legitimate (a page load makes 20 requests at once):
```ts
const LUA_TB = `
local key      = KEYS[1]
local now      = tonumber(ARGV[1])
local capacity = tonumber(ARGV[2])   -- max burst
local rate     = tonumber(ARGV[3])   -- tokens per millisecond

local data   = redis.call('HMGET', key, 't', 'ts')
local tokens = tonumber(data[1])
local ts     = tonumber(data[2])
if tokens == nil then tokens = capacity; ts = now end

local delta = now - ts
if delta < 0 then delta = 0 end               -- clock moved backwards: do not grant tokens
tokens = math.min(capacity, tokens + delta * rate)

local allowed, retry = 0, 0
if tokens >= 1 then
  allowed, tokens = 1, tokens - 1
else
  retry = math.ceil((1 - tokens) / rate)
end

redis.call('HSET', key, 't', tostring(tokens), 'ts', now)
redis.call('PEXPIRE', key, math.ceil(capacity / rate) + 1000)
return { allowed, tostring(tokens), retry }
`;

async function tokenBucket(userId: string, { capacity = 20, perSecond = 5 } = {}) {
  const res = await redis.eval(LUA_TB, 1, `rl:tb:${userId}`,
    Date.now(), capacity, perSecond / 1000) as [number, string, number];
  return { allowed: res[0] === 1, tokens: Number(res[1]), retryAfterMs: res[2] };
}
```
Notes that a senior candidate volunteers: pass `Date.now()` **from the application**, not from `redis.call('TIME')` inside the script, so you control clock skew across clients and can unit-test it; the returned values are **truncated to integers by Redis when converting a Lua number to a reply**, so return `tostring(tokens)` and parse in TypeScript (or use scaled integers such as milli-tokens and divide in the client); the script is atomic, which is the entire point — a read-modify-write in TypeScript has a race; the `PEXPIRE` is proportional to the time needed to refill the bucket, so idle keys self-clean; and for Cluster, a single key is fine, but a multi-key script needs a hash tag.

**Leaky bucket** — models the *output* rate, not the input rate. A queue plus a fixed drain rate of 1/latency seconds; each item exits the bucket at a steady rate, so a burst is absorbed and released smoothly. Implementations: a zset of scheduled release times plus a Lua script that admits only if `now - first_scheduled >= interval`, or a token bucket with `capacity = 1` and no burst (which is a leaky bucket in everything but name). Use it when you are protecting a fragile downstream that cannot absorb bursts at all, e.g. sending SMS, calling a rate-limited third-party API, or writing to a partner endpoint with a contractual limit. The cost is latency: a burst that is admitted is queued, so the last request waits.

**Cluster and multi-region caveats for rate limiting:**
- A single Redis instance caps at ~100k ops/s; above that, shard the limiter keys across a cluster. Because each key is independent, this is easy — but the *global* limit is no longer exact; it becomes per-shard, so decide whether your SLA requires a global or per-shard limit.
- Multi-region requires either a home-region limiter (extra cross-region latency on the check, and a hole if the region is down) or a per-region limiter (limit multiplied by regions — state it explicitly, and multiply your per-region limit by the number of regions so the global limit holds).
- Fail open or fail closed, deliberately. For a login endpoint, fail closed. For a read endpoint, fail open and count it in metrics. Make it a config flag, and alert on it, because a fail-open flag left on during an incident is a very expensive mistake.
- Counters drift under eviction. With `allkeys-lru` at capacity, limiter keys get evicted and every client effectively gets a fresh quota. Give the limiter instance `noeviction` and a headroom-based memory alert.

**Sessions in Redis.** The standard shape: a random 256-bit session id in an `HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Lax` cookie, with the data in `session:<id>` as a hash, `EXPIRE`d and refreshed on activity (sliding expiration). Good: you can revoke instantly, share sessions across app instances, and set an absolute plus an idle timeout. Watch out for: session fixation (always regenerate the id on privilege change, i.e. on login), the revocation list growing if you blacklist JWTs (prefer Redis sessions or short-lived tokens with a refresh token), PII in a session store is a compliance surface, and logout-everywhere needs a per-user session index (`user:<id>:sessions`) with a TTL.

**When NOT to use Redis as a source of truth.** This is the honest senior answer, and being able to give it unprompted is a differentiator:
1. **You need to lose nothing.** Redis is in-memory and its durability is a background job. If a region dies and a replica promotes, you can lose the last second (AOF `everysec`) or the last acknowledged writes (async replication). `min-replicas-to-write` + `WAIT` narrows the window; it does not close it.
2. **You need transactions with real isolation.** `MULTI/EXEC` is not a transaction in the ACID sense. Multi-key invariants need a single document (hash with fields) plus a Lua script, or a real database.
3. **Your data is large or growing unboundedly.** Once you are storing tens of GB, or a value grows past a few hundred KB, you are running a database on a cache: no indexing, no partial reads, memory pressure, rewrite amplification.
4. **You need secondary queries.** "All users created last week with a plan of X" is a `SCAN` of your entire keyspace. That is a full table scan with worse tooling.
5. **It is the system of record for money or anything legally immutable.** Financial records, audit logs, and medical data want an append-only, transactional, backed-up store. Use Redis as a cache in front of it.
6. **Correctness depends on a TTL.** If a key silently disappearing causes data loss, a cache eviction policy is your data-loss policy.
7. **The write is idempotency-sensitive.** Redis gives you `SETNX` for a coarse lock, but a crashed process between "set the key" and "do the work" leaves you with a half-finished state you must reconcile.
8. **The team cannot operate it.** A stateful, in-memory, single-writer-per-shard service with a fork-based persistence model is a different operational commitment than Postgres. If nobody on the team knows what `lastsave` and `INFO memory` mean, do not make it authoritative.

The rule of thumb: **Redis is authoritative only for data that is either (a) reconstructible from elsewhere, or (b) ephemeral by definition — sessions, carts, rate limits, locks, idempotency keys, leaderboards, and caches.**

**Questions**
1. Which Redis data structure would you use for a leaderboard, and why not a sorted list?
2. What does Redis's single-threaded execution model buy you, and what is the danger?
3. Why is `KEYS *` banned, and what replaces it?
4. What is pipelining, when does it help, and what does it not fix?
5. What does `MULTI/EXEC` guarantee, and what does it not?
6. RDB vs AOF `everysec` vs AOF `always`: what do you lose in each?
7. What problem does Sentinel solve, and what does it not?
8. Explain Redis Cluster hash slots, and what `MOVED` and `ASK` mean.
9. What is `CROSSSLOT` and what is the fix?
10. List the `maxmemory` policies and when each is correct.
11. What happens to a write when `maxmemory` is reached and the policy is `noeviction`?
12. Why must limiter instances not share a node with a cache?
13. DynamoDB: what are the two keys, and what can a `Query` never do?
14. Design a DynamoDB key schema for tenant-scoped orders and their items.
15. What is a `scan`, why is it a production bug, and what is it good for?
16. What is a hot partition, what is the hard limit, and list four fixes.
17. LSI vs GSI: cost, consistency, and what each is for.
18. What does `ConsistentRead: true` cost, and where is it not allowed?
19. Write a conditional update that cannot double-spend a balance.
20. How do you make a `PutItem` idempotent?
21. Provisioned vs on-demand capacity, with the spike cost.
22. What is TTL good for, and what is it not good for?
23. What problem do DynamoDB Streams solve, and what do consumers owe you?
24. What does DAX do and not do?
25. Compare the six caching patterns. Which is the default and why?
26. Why TTL jitter, and how much?
27. Three ways to stop a cache stampede, and the cost of each.
28. Write the release Lua script for a Redis lock and explain why the token check matters.
29. What is Redlock unsafe against, and what is a fencing token?
30. When are Postgres advisory locks the better lock?
31. Contrast the five rate-limiting algorithms on memory, precision, and burst.
32. Why does a fixed window let 2x the limit through, and what is the boundary case?
33. Why does a sliding window log need a unique member id?
34. Explain the sliding window counter's estimate and its worst-case error.
35. Why does a token bucket return `tostring` from Lua?
36. What is a leaky bucket, and what is it protecting that a token bucket is not?
37. Rate limiting in Cluster and multi-region: what breaks?
38. Fail open or fail closed: how do you decide?
39. Where do sessions belong, and what is session fixation?
40. Give five reasons not to make Redis a source of truth.

**Model answer, Q31:** "Five algorithms, and the decision is really memory versus precision versus whether bursts are legitimate. A fixed window counter is one integer per key and one round trip, but it is wrong at the boundary: with 100 per minute a client can send 100 at the end of one window and 100 at the start of the next, so 200 requests in two milliseconds. That is a real exploit and easy to detect. A sliding window log stores every request timestamp in a sorted set, trims anything older than the window, and counts the remainder, so it is exact and perfectly smooth, but the cost is proportional to the limit — at a million active users with a ten-request limit that is ten million zset entries, which is why I would not use it on a public API. A sliding window counter keeps two counters, the current window and the previous one, and estimates the count in the trailing window as current plus previous weighted by the fraction of the previous window still inside the range; it costs almost nothing and its error decays linearly, with a worst case of roughly one previous-window count, so a hundred-per-minute limit might see about a hundred and ten. A token bucket stores a level and a timestamp, refills continuously at the rate, and allows a burst up to the capacity, which is what I want for a browser page that fires twenty requests at once, and the continuous refill means a client recovers gradually rather than all at once on a window edge. A leaky bucket is the output-rate model: it admits a burst into a queue and releases at a steady rate, so the downstream never sees a burst, and the price is queueing latency. My default is token bucket for a user-facing API and sliding window counter when the limit is small and fairness matters; I would use the log only for an expensive endpoint with a tiny limit, and I would use leaky bucket to protect a downstream that cannot absorb bursts at all. In all cases the script is one atomic Lua call, and in Cluster the keys are hash-tagged so a multi-key script stays in one slot."

**Model answer, Q40:** "Five reasons. First, durability: Redis is in-memory, so an AOF with `everysec` loses about a second and async replication loses acknowledged writes on failover — `min-replicas-to-write` and `WAIT` narrow that but never close it. Second, `MULTI/EXEC` is not ACID: no rollback, no snapshot isolation, so a multi-key invariant needs a single hash plus a Lua script, which is a real modelling constraint rather than a transaction. Third, size: past tens of gigabytes, or once a value gets big, I am running a database on a cache with no secondary indexes and a rewrite problem. Fourth, query expressiveness: 'every user created last week on the enterprise plan' is a full scan of my keyspace. Fifth, operations: an in-memory single-writer-per-shard service with fork-based persistence is a real commitment. My rule is that Redis is authoritative only for data that is reconstructible elsewhere or ephemeral by definition: sessions, carts, rate limits, locks, idempotency keys, leaderboards, and caches. Anything I would be upset to explain to an auditor lives in Postgres."

**Mistakes:** using `KEYS` in production code; storing large values in Redis; mixing cache and authoritative data in one instance with `allkeys-lru`; assuming `MULTI/EXEC` rolls back; using a fixed TTL for every key; releasing a lock with a plain `DEL`; believing Redlock gives you exactly-once side effects; using a fixed window for a hard quota; passing `Date.now()` from inside the Lua script and losing control of clock skew; assuming DynamoDB `Query` can filter across partitions; expecting an eventually consistent read to return a just-written item; using `FilterExpression` to "reduce" `Scan` cost (it does not); putting high-cardinality, unbounded values in a limiter or cache keyspace without bounds.

**Mini task:** implement all five rate limiters in TypeScript against a local Redis, plus an in-process variant, and write a load test that hammers the boundary: send exactly `limit` requests at the end of a fixed window, then `limit` at the start of the next, and plot which algorithms let 2x through. Then convert the token bucket to use a distributed lock plus a "leader refreshes, followers wait" pattern, and show the failure mode when the leader dies. Finally, set `maxmemory 64mb` on a scratch instance, fill it, and watch each eviction policy do its thing.

---

# 6.21 Wide-Column Stores: Cassandra
Advanced · **Should-know**

**Definition:** A store organised as rows grouped into partitions, where a read is "give me this partition and this range of clustering keys". Writes are local appends, and the query surface is intentionally tiny.

**Positioning:** Cassandra is the answer to a very specific question — "we ingest an enormous number of writes across many datacentres and we know exactly the handful of queries we will ever run." It is a bad answer to almost every other question, and knowing that boundary is the whole skill.

## 6.21.1 The data model that makes it fast

```
CREATE TABLE time_series (
    device_id  uuid,
    day        date,
    ts         timestamp,
    value      double,
    tags       map<text, text>,
    PRIMARY KEY ((device_id), day, ts)
) WITH CLUSTERING ORDER BY (day DESC, ts DESC)
   AND default_time_to_live = 2592000;   -- 30 days
```

- **Partition key** (`device_id`) chooses which node owns the data. All rows with the same partition key live on the same set of replicas, so a query touching only the partition key needs no cross-node coordination.
- **Clustering columns** (`day`, `ts`) order rows *within* a partition and let you slice a range: `WHERE device_id = ? AND ts > ?` reads one contiguous span.
- **Rules that follow:** every query must specify the partition key. The clustering columns must be constrained in prefix order — you can range on `ts` only if you also constrain `day`, because the data is sorted by `(day, ts)`. Partition size is the tuning parameter that matters most: a 1GB partition makes reads slow and unrepairable, a 10MB partition wastes memory on the read path.
- **Static columns** hold a value that is not per-row but per-partition (a device's metadata). One write, cached with the partition, no extra row.
- **Collections** (`set`, `list`, `map`) exist and are convenient for small, bounded, rarely-queried data. They are a well-known footgun: multi-cell reads, poor compaction, and a poor fit for queries that need to search inside. Use them for a handful of tags, not for a list of 10,000 events.
- **Materialized views** are the escape valve for "I need a second query shape": a view with a different primary key, written asynchronously from the base table. It is eventual, it costs double the write path, and it is still a whole table you must model. In practice, most Cassandra schemas use several *tables* per entity rather than views.

**One table per query, denormalized.** Because there are no joins and no arbitrary indexes, the pattern is:
```
time_series_by_device   ((device_id), day, ts)        -- the obvious query
time_series_by_day      ((day), device_id, ts)        -- daily rollup view
time_series_by_region   ((region, day), device_id, ts) -- cross-device rollup
```
This is a *good* design, and it is also the design that will terrify someone who learned relational modelling. The cost is storage multiplication and the fact that every query shape must be anticipated — you cannot ask a question you did not plan for.

## 6.21.2 `ALLOW FILTERING` and secondary indexes: the traps

- `SELECT * FROM t WHERE partition = 'x' AND non_key_column = 'y' ALLOW FILTERING` reads **every row of the partition**, discards most of them, and returns the rest. The coordinator needs heap for the whole partition, the read time is proportional to partition size, and a concurrent write storm makes it worse. It is a debugging tool, and in production it is a self-inflicted outage on a schedule.
- **Secondary indexes** (2i) do exist, and they are not a general answer: they are local to each node (so a query with a secondary-index predicate fans out to all replicas and merges), they can return inconsistent results under concurrency, they do not scale with data volume, and they multiply write cost. Use them for a genuinely low-selectivity filter; otherwise denormalize.
- SASI indexes and the newer storage-attached indexes improve on some of this but do not change the philosophy: *design for the query you know*.

## 6.21.3 Consistency, quorum maths, and repair

- **Levels:** `ONE`, `TWO`, `THREE`, `QUORUM`, `ALL`, `EACH_QUORUM`, `LOCAL_QUORUM`, `LOCAL_ONE`, `LOCAL_SERIAL`, `SERIAL`.
- **The arithmetic you must be able to do out loud:** for replication factor `N`, `QUORUM = floor(N/2) + 1`. With `N = 3`: `ONE` = 1, `QUORUM` = 2, `ALL` = 3.
- **The anti-entropy rule:** for a read/write to be *strongly* consistent you need `R + W > N`. With `N = 3`, `W = 2` and `R = 2` guarantees the read and write sets overlap in at least one replica, so a read cannot miss a committed write.
- **`W > N/2` is what stops lost updates and phantom data** from an acknowledged write that is then "unwritten" by a stale coordinator. `W = 1` with `N = 3` means two replicas can disagree with the ack forever (until a repair), and a client that got a success has no guarantee.
- **Stale reads** are legitimate and cheap at `ONE`; the trap is using `ONE` for a read that must reflect a just-acknowledged write. The fix is a **read-your-writes token**: a per-client monotonically increasing timestamp captured on write (`UPDATE ... USING TIMESTAMP ?`), then a read with a "cluster" or "client" timestamp filter that refuses anything older. Cassandra's `USING TIMESTAMP` is also how you do last-write-wins conflict resolution, and the clock is the client's, so **clock skew becomes your problem** — bound it with a coarse per-client timestamp, and be honest that skewed clocks can lose writes.
- **Read repair** happens on reads at `QUORUM` or higher: mismatched replicas are fixed in the background. `ONE` reads skip it, so a `ONE`-read-heavy workload accumulates divergence; that is why `nodetool repair` is a scheduled, non-optional operational task.
- **Hinted handoff** is how a write survives a replica being down: the coordinator stores a hint and delivers it when the node returns. It is bounded by the down-time you can tolerate, and after `gc_grace` (default 10 days) hints and tombstones are dropped, so a replica that is down too long needs a full `nodetool repair` or a rebuild from a snapshot.
- **Tombstones and `gc_grace`:** a delete is a tombstone, not a removal, and it is a *write* that must be broadcast. Heavy delete workloads create large tombstone counts; a read touching too many tombstones fails with `tombstone count exceeds threshold`. The `gc_grace` window is the price of a distributed delete, and lowering it for write-heavy data can resurrect deleted rows on a late-arriving replica.
- **LWT (lightweight transactions):** `INSERT ... IF NOT EXISTS` gives you a compare-and-set *within a single partition* at `SERIAL` consistency, at the cost of roughly two round trips (a prepare phase and a commit phase) and a paxos-like agreement. It is the correct tool for "this username must be unique" and "check-then-act on a counter". It is *not* a transaction across partitions and it is *not* free: heavy LWT usage serialises on the partition and destroys throughput. Use it where a uniqueness constraint genuinely cannot be expressed otherwise.

## 6.21.4 Compaction, TTLs, and operations

- **Compaction** is where SSTables get merged and where reads get fast. **STCS** (size-tiered) is the default: good for write-heavy and uniform data, bad for read latency spikes and for data with uneven per-partition sizes. **LCS** (leveled) gives lower read amplification at the cost of write amplification and more compaction work. **TWCS** (time-window) is the right choice for time-series data with TTLs, because it buckets by time window and drops whole expired buckets cheaply. Choosing the compaction strategy for the access pattern (write-heavy vs read-heavy, uniform vs skewed, TTL or not) is a genuinely senior decision.
- **Compression** (LZ4 by default, or a heavier algorithm) trades CPU for space and I/O, and it is nearly always worth it.
- **TTL** is per row (`USING TTL ?`) or per table (`default_time_to_live`). Expiry is not exact: it happens during compaction of the bucket, so a row can outlive its TTL by minutes or hours, and it generates tombstones that participate in `gc_grace`. TTLs also make a node unreachable for a period ("UNREACHABLE" for 2x TTL after an extended outage) because re-inserting a row whose tombstone is still around can resurrect data. Plan node-down time against your TTL.
- **A write path worth understanding:** client → coordinator → memtable (in memory) → SSTable on disk, with an in-memory row cache and a bloom filter per SSTable to avoid reading files that cannot contain the key. Bloom filters are why a missing key in a large partition is cheap, and why `ALLOW FILTERING` is not: a filter on a *non-key* column has no bloom filter to consult.
- **Capacity and distribution:** virtual nodes spread a token's ownership across many nodes so adding a node does not require moving a huge contiguous range. Reads follow the token ring; the coordinator is chosen by the partitioner. Nothing is a single point of failure, but every write is replicated `N` ways, so the storage amplification is real.

## 6.21.5 When Cassandra fits

**Fits:**
- Very high write ingest where a write is a local append: IoT telemetry, event/click logs, audit trails, messaging metadata.
- A small, *known* set of queries, stated in advance, with a bounded partition size.
- Time-ordered data with retention (telemetry, activity feeds), especially with TWCS and TTLs.
- Multi-datacentre replication with region-local reads and no cross-region coordination on the write path.
- A team that accepts denormalization as a permanent architectural commitment.

**Does not fit:**
- Ad-hoc exploration, reporting, or "one more question" work. There is no join, no group-by across partitions, and no ad-hoc index.
- Read-heavy relational workloads with many secondary lookups.
- Transactions spanning entities (a transfer between two accounts). Use a single ledger row, or a relational store.
- Small data. A single-node Cassandra cluster has more operational surface than a Postgres table, and no benefit.
- A team that wants transactions. Full stop.

**Interview framing:** "I would reach for Cassandra when the question is 'how do I absorb 200k writes per second per node across three regions, and I know I will only ever ask three queries, and the data has a two-year retention'. If I cannot enumerate the queries, I would not use it, because the design *is* the queries."

**Questions**
1. What is a partition key versus a clustering column, and how do they differ in a query?
2. What happens to `WHERE device_id = ? AND ts > ?` if `day` is a clustering column before `ts`?
3. Why must every query specify the partition key?
4. How do you choose a partition size, and what goes wrong at 1GB?
5. What is the "one table per query" pattern, and what does it cost?
6. What are static columns for?
7. Why is `ALLOW FILTERING` dangerous? Explain the mechanism.
8. What are the problems with secondary indexes in Cassandra?
9. Define the consistency levels and compute `QUORUM` for `N = 5`.
10. State the `R + W > N` rule and what breaks without it.
11. What is read repair, and what happens if you only ever read at `ONE`?
12. What is hinted handoff, and what is its time limit?
13. What are tombstones, what is `gc_grace`, and what is `UNREACHABLE`?
14. What are lightweight transactions, what do they cost, and what is the one thing they cannot do?
15. How do you get read-your-writes in a system with `ONE` reads?
16. What is `USING TIMESTAMP` and what does it move onto your application?
17. Compare STCS, LCS, and TWCS. When is TWCS right?
18. What does a TTL cost you besides "the row goes away"?
19. Explain the write path, including the bloom filter, and where read latency comes from.
20. Give three workloads Cassandra fits and three it does not.
21. How do you count users in Cassandra when you cannot `GROUP BY`?
22. What is the difference between virtual nodes and classic token ranges?

**Model answer, Q10:** "The rule is that for a read to be guaranteed to see any acknowledged write, the read replica set and the write replica set must overlap, and that happens when reads plus writes exceeds the replication factor. With three replicas, writing at quorum and reading at quorum gives four against three, so there is at least one node in both sets and the read cannot miss the write. The failure case is instructive: write at `ONE`, read at `ONE`. The client writes to replica A, is acknowledged, then reads from replica B, which has not received the write yet, and gets stale data from a write that was reported as successful. Worse, if the coordinator for a later write is B, it can hand out a value that contradicts A, and because nothing repairs without a quorum read, the divergence persists. That is why the choice of write consistency is the durability decision, and why read consistency is a separate, per-query decision: the write path's `W` is what you promise, and the read path's `R` is what your users will actually see."

**Mistakes:** designing the schema around entities instead of queries; forgetting clustering columns must be constrained in prefix order; using `ALLOW FILTERING` in a "temporary" fix; assuming a unique constraint is free (it needs an LWT or a denormalized uniqueness table); treating a TTL as precise; ignoring `gc_grace` when planning node downtime; believing `W = 1` is fine because "the coordinator acked"; assuming secondary indexes are equivalent to a relational index.

**Mini task:** load 50M rows into a three-node cluster with a 10-day TTL. Measure insert throughput at `ONE`, `QUORUM`, and `LOCAL_QUORUM`; then measure read p99 at each level and the effect of compaction strategy on read latency. Kill a node mid-write and observe hinted handoff, then leave it down past `gc_grace` and see what a repair has to do. Finally, write a query you *want* but did not model, and time the `ALLOW FILTERING` version against a materialized-view version.

---

# 6.22 Graph Databases
Advanced · **Should-know**

**Definition:** A store whose primary abstraction is nodes, typed relationships, and properties, designed so that *variable-depth traversal* is the cheap operation rather than the accidental one.

**The honest opening.** Most interview "graph" questions are not graph problems. If the traversal depth is fixed at one or two hops, a relational join table with a well-chosen index is faster, better documented, transactional, and cheaper (see 6.1–6.17 for join and index planning). A graph store earns its keep when **depth is unbounded or unknown at design time**, when the relationship itself carries data, or when the question is inherently about paths, neighbourhoods, or connectivity.

Genuinely graph-shaped: fraud rings (who shares devices, addresses, and card BINs with whom, to any depth), an org chart or supply chain, dependency and blast-radius analysis, recommendation via multi-hop similarity, identity resolution, network and service topology, permissions with inherited roles.

Not graph-shaped: "orders joined to customers", "the top 10 products by revenue", "users with more than 5 orders", "find the shortest delivery path between two warehouses" (a graph *can* do this, but so can a recursive CTE over an edge table, and the edge table might be the only 500 rows you have).

## 6.22.1 Modelling

```
CREATE CONSTRAINT user_email IF NOT EXISTS FOR (n:User) REQUIRE n.email IS UNIQUE;
CREATE INDEX user_id IF NOT EXISTS FOR (n:User) ON (n.id);

CREATE INDEX follows_focus IF NOT EXISTS FOR ()-[r:FOLLOWS]-() ON (r.since);
```

- **Nodes** carry identity and a label; **edges** carry direction, type, and usually properties (`since`, `weight`, `role`, `validFrom`). Labels should be few and meaningful; a property that is really a subclass is a label. A node with a type field (`kind: 'a' | 'b'`) is harder to index and constrain than two labels.
- **Every relationship needs an identity of its own** if you will ever attach properties or query it independently, and you need *both* directions queried, because an undirected traversal in a directed model requires two patterns or a flag. Modelling every edge as both `(a)-[:X]->(b)` and `(b)-[:X]->(a)` is a common and effective denormalization.
- **Edge properties must not be the only copy of the truth.** A `FRIENDSHIP` edge with `weight` derived from interactions is a cache; it needs a rebuild path.
- **Nodes with too many edges (god nodes)** destroy traversal performance and are usually a modelling smell — an "everything" supernode usually wants to be a set, a property, or a hyperedge split into an intermediate node.
- **Hyperedges** (a relationship between a *set* of things: "these three people were in the same transaction") need an intermediate node, because a relationship in a property graph connects exactly two nodes.
- **Delete semantics:** deleting a node requires deleting or nullifying every incident edge. In Neo4j that is `DETACH DELETE`, and the "orphan edge" question is a real interview detail.

## 6.22.2 Cypher and traversal cost

```cypher
// 1-hop: usually a fine relational join
MATCH (a:User {id: $userId})-[:FOLLOWS]->(b:User)
RETURN b.id, b.name LIMIT 50;

// variable depth with a hop cap and relationship predicates
MATCH path = (a:User {id: $id})-[:FOLLOWS*1..4]->(b:User)
WHERE all(r IN relationships(path) WHERE r.since > date('2024-01-01'))
RETURN b.id, length(path) AS depth
ORDER BY depth, b.followers DESC LIMIT 100;

// shortest path, weighted
MATCH (a:Depot {id: $from}), (b:Depot {id: $to}),
      p = shortestPath((a)-[:ROUTE*..6 {active: true}]-(b))
RETURN [n IN nodes(p) | n.id] AS path, reduce(t = 0, r IN relationships(p) | t + r.minutes) AS minutes;

// neighbourhood for a recommendation: people who follow people I follow, minus people I follow
MATCH (me:User {id: $id})-[:FOLLOWS]->(f)-[:FOLLOWS]->(cand:User)
WHERE NOT (me)-[:FOLLOWS]->(cand)
RETURN cand.id, count(*) AS mutual
ORDER BY mutual DESC LIMIT 20;

// constraint-driven unique create
MERGE (a:User {email: $email}) ON CREATE SET a.createdAt = datetime()
SET a.lastSeenAt = datetime()
RETURN a;
```

- **Cost model:** a variable-length pattern is a breadth- or depth-first expansion with pruning. `*1..4` over a node with 5,000 edges is up to 5^4 expansions before the LIMIT helps you. Mitigation: always bound depth, always `LIMIT`, put the selective predicate inside the traversal (`-[r:FOLLOWS*1..3 WHERE r.since > ...]-`) rather than filtering after, and put the LIMIT early where the engine can push it down.
- **Plans are cached per query shape**, so parameterised Cypher is fast and string-built Cypher is not. This is the same lesson as prepared statements everywhere else.
- **Property existence in a `WHERE` on a hot traversal prevents cheap index use**; index the node property you start from, and index edges you filter on.
- **Aggregation in Cypher is done in the engine**, which is genuinely nice for graph-shaped aggregations that SQL cannot express cleanly.

## 6.22.3 Relational alternatives that often win

```sql
-- A join table is the right model for a fixed 1-hop relationship
CREATE TABLE follows (
  follower_id bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  followee_id bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  created_at   timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
  PRIMARY KEY (follower_id, followee_id)
);
CREATE INDEX ON follows (followee_id);           -- reverse direction
-- A user with 10k followers:
SELECT u.* FROM users u JOIN follows f ON f.followee_id = $1
WHERE f.follower_id IN (SELECT id FROM users WHERE ...) LIMIT 50;
```

```sql
-- Variable depth via a recursive CTE, with a hard depth cap and a cycle guard
WITH RECURSIVE reach(user_id, depth, path) AS (
  SELECT $1::bigint, 0, ARRAY[$1::bigint]
  UNION ALL
  SELECT f.followee_id, r.depth + 1, r.path || f.followee_id
  FROM reach r
  JOIN follows f ON f.follower_id = r.user_id
  WHERE r.depth < 3
    AND NOT f.followee_id = ANY(r.path)          -- no revisits
)
SELECT user_id, MIN(depth) AS depth FROM reach GROUP BY user_id;
```
Cross-reference 6.1–6.17 for the recursive CTE, the `UNION ALL` growth problem, and the index that makes each iteration fast (`(follower_id)` is the prefix, which the primary key already gives you).

| Dimension | Graph store | Postgres join table + CTE | Document store (`$graphLookup`) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Unbounded-depth traversal | Purpose-built, fastest | Works; iterative and index-sensitive; plan cache pressure at depth | Bounded by `maxDepth`; a nested-loop scan per level |
| Transactions | Yes, ACID, but cross-graph-cluster needs a distributed transaction | Excellent; advisory locks, serializable, constraints | Cross-document only, expensive |
| Constraints | Uniqueness constraints on node properties; no foreign keys, no `CHECK` | Full | Application-level |
| Reporting and aggregates | Weak; you will export to SQL anyway | Excellent; window functions, CTEs, the whole OLAP story | Adequate via aggregation pipelines |
| Operational familiarity | New engine, new backup and monitoring story | Ubiquitous tooling | Ubiquitous-ish |
| Best when | Paths and neighbourhoods are the product | Relationship depth is bounded, or data is small | One or two hops of a tree/hierarchy lookup |

**Interview framing:** "I ask two questions before reaching for a graph. Is the depth unbounded? Does the relationship itself carry data? If both are no, a join table with a composite primary key and a reverse index is faster, transactional, and something the team already knows how to run. If yes, a graph store removes an entire class of problem — variable-depth traversal and path queries that would be several recursive CTEs and a mess of application code."

**Questions**
1. When is a graph database genuinely the right tool? Give three domains and one anti-example.
2. What is the cost of a variable-length traversal, and how do you control it?
3. Model `User -[:FOLLOWS {since}]-> User` and list the indexes you would create.
4. How do you model an edge with properties that you also need to query by?
5. What is a hyperedge, and how do you represent it?
6. What is a "god node", and what does it do to performance?
7. Write the Cypher for a 3-hop neighbour recommendation excluding existing friends.
8. Write a Cypher query for the shortest weighted path between two nodes. What does `reduce` do?
9. What does `MERGE` do differently from `CREATE`, and what is the concurrency hazard?
10. `DETACH DELETE` versus `DELETE` — what is the difference and why does it matter?
11. Write the recursive CTE version of a 3-hop follow traversal, with a cycle guard.
12. When does the recursive CTE win over a graph engine?
13. What is plan caching, and why does string-built Cypher hurt?
14. How do you decide edge direction, and what do you do when queries need both?
15. How do you handle the fact that a graph store has no foreign keys?
16. How do you run reports and aggregations on a graph store?
17. What does MongoDB's `$graphLookup` do well, and where does `maxDepth` betray you?

**Model answer, Q1:** "I reach for a graph when two things are true at once: the traversal depth is unbounded or unknown at design time, and the relationship itself is a first-class thing with data on it. Fraud ring detection is the canonical case — you ask who shares a device, a billing address, or a card BIN with whom, and you do not know in advance how far the ring extends; the query is a path query, and every hop is a comparison. Dependency and blast-radius analysis is the same shape: given this package, what breaks if it goes away. Recommendations by mutual connections are a three-hop traversal with an exclusion predicate, which is awkward in SQL and one line in Cypher. The anti-example is 'customers joined to their most recent order', which is a one-hop join with an index and a `DISTINCT ON`; a graph store there is overhead, loses foreign keys and transactions, and adds an engine to operate. So my rule is: fixed depth and no edge data means a join table, and I say that in the interview even when the question is dressed up in graph vocabulary."

**Mistakes:** using a graph store for one- and two-hop joins; unbounded `*` traversals; filtering after the traversal instead of inside it; string-concatenated Cypher; storing the only copy of data on an edge; not bounding depth; assuming a graph store gives you transactions across the whole graph, or foreign keys, or good reporting; forgetting that deleting a node leaves orphan edges unless you `DETACH DELETE`.

**Mini task:** take 100k synthetic users with a follows graph and answer the same three questions in Postgres (join table plus recursive CTE) and in a graph engine: (a) mutual connections of a user, (b) users within 3 hops who do not follow anyone I follow, (c) the size of the connected component. Record query times, memory, and the code length. Then try the same with depth unbounded to see exactly where both approaches stop being acceptable.

---

# 6.23 Time-Series and Vector Stores
Advanced · **Should-know**

**Definition:** Two specialised families — one that optimises time-window reads and retention over append-only measurements, and one that answers "what is nearest to this vector" approximately and fast.

## 6.23.1 TimescaleDB: hypertables and continuous aggregates

```sql
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS timescaledb;

-- a hypertable is one logical table auto-partitioned on the time column
SELECT create_hypertable('sensor_reading', by_range('ts'), if_not_exists => TRUE);

-- space partitioning on a second dimension buys parallelism and index locality
SELECT add_dimension('sensor_reading', by_hash('device_id', 8));

ALTER TABLE sensor_reading SET (
  timescaledb.compress,
  timescaledb.compress_segmentby = 'device_id',
  timescaledb.compress_orderby   = 'ts DESC'
);

CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW sensor_hourly
WITH (timescaledb.continuous) AS
SELECT device_id,
       time_bucket('1 hour', ts) AS bucket,
       avg(value) AS avg_value,
       max(value) AS max_value
FROM sensor_reading
WHERE ts > now() - INTERVAL '7 days'
GROUP BY device_id, bucket
WITH NO DATA;                       -- build later, so the migration is fast

SELECT add_continuous_aggregate_policy('sensor_hourly',
  start_offset      => INTERVAL '30 days',   -- how far back it stays current
  end_offset        => INTERVAL '1 hour',    -- leave the last hour raw for late data
  schedule_interval => INTERVAL '10 minutes');

SELECT add_retention_policy('sensor_reading', INTERVAL '90 days');
```

- **What a hypertable gives you:** automatic chunking by time so the index stays small and recent, chunk-local compression with a segment-by column (dictionaries for the low-cardinality part and delta-of-delta time encoding for the rest), retention as a `DROP CHUNK` instead of a mass `DELETE`, and the ability to attach custom retention and compression policies.
- **`start_offset` and `end_offset` matter for correctness, not just cost.** An aggregate that refreshes every bucket up to "now" will keep recomputing the incomplete current bucket, and late-arriving data will be lost. Leaving `end_offset` at a few buckets means the most recent window is read from raw data, so out-of-order writes land correctly.
- **Continuous aggregates are incrementally refreshed** — only the changed region is recomputed — which is what makes them cheap. They are read-only; refresh them with `CALL refresh_continuous_aggregate(...)`. They are not real-time-accurate to the millisecond, and they *cannot* be joined against a raw hypertable in the same query in some versions, which is a real limitation to know before you design around them.
- **`time_bucket_gapfill`** interpolates missing buckets so a chart does not draw a line through a device outage as if it were zero. Reporting a gap as 0 is a correctness bug that dashboards love to hide.
- **Watch out for:** queries that join a hypertable to a normal table (the planner often does a hash join over the *entire* hypertable unless you filter by time first), the absence of a primary key in the Postgres sense on a hypertable (a unique index must include all partitioning columns), and `ORDER BY ts DESC LIMIT 20` on a huge hypertable if the chunk pruning is not working. Always add an explicit time predicate and confirm chunk pruning with `EXPLAIN`.
- **Scale-out path:** continuous aggregates roll up; beyond one node, use a columnar store (ClickHouse) or a warehouse. The honest answer is that Postgres-plus-Timescale is excellent up to a few hundred million rows per hypertable with good time predicates, and a columnar OLAP engine is better past that.

## 6.23.2 Prometheus TSDB: the cardinality trap

- A **series** is a unique label set, and every series needs a chunk in memory, an index entry, and file space. The label set is the primary key, so **labels are columns and their values are the cardinality multiplier**: `job`, `instance`, and `status` give you 3 dimensions; adding `user_id` turns "a few thousand series" into "tens of millions" and the TSDB falls over. The rule: **never use a high-cardinality value as a label.** Push the id into the *value* as a field, or aggregate it out, or drop it.
  Classic offenders: `user_id`, `request_id`, `trace_id`, `error_message`, `full_url` with an id in the query string, `version` per deploy, `email`, `sql` text, `pod` name when you also have `deployment` and `namespace` at high churn.
  Governance tools that actually work: a metric naming and label policy in code review, a lint step against an allow-list of label names, and dashboards of series-per-metric so a regression is visible in review.
- **The head block lives in memory/WAL** and the checkpoint controls it: `--storage.tsdb.max-block-duration`, the head chunk range, and `--storage.tsdb.head-chunks-write-queue-size` interact so that a burst of series can OOM the process. Cap series with `max_label_names_per_series` and monitor `prometheus_tsdb_head_series`.
- **The scrape target count multiplies series**: `series ≈ targets × jobs × label combinations`. Doubling the fleet doubles memory, which is why horizontal sharding (one Prometheus per team, or a Thanos/Cortex-style store) is the standard answer.
- **Recording rules** pre-aggregate so dashboards query cheap series, and **downsampling** keeps long-range resolution affordable. Both are the correct answer to "we need 13 months of data" and both cost a rule-evaluation and a compaction story.
- **`histogram_quantile` over a high-cardinality `le` bucket set** is expensive; recording rules that pre-aggregate the histogram into a quantile reduce the load.
- **Remote write** is how you get durability and long retention (to a receiver or an object store), at the cost of batching, ordering not being guaranteed across shards, and duplicate series on a retry. Version the write request to avoid clobbering newer samples with out-of-order ones.
- Contrast: InfluxDB's line protocol makes the same point differently — **tags** are indexed and are your series key, **fields** are values; putting a high-cardinality value in a tag is the same mistake under a different name.

## 6.23.3 Vector search with pgvector

```sql
CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS vector;

-- 1536-dim embeddings from a 1536-dim model
CREATE TABLE doc_chunk (
  id          bigserial PRIMARY KEY,
  doc_id      bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES doc(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  tenant_id   bigint NOT NULL,
  content     text   NOT NULL,
  embedding   vector(1536) NOT NULL,
  model       text   NOT NULL,          -- pin the model that produced these vectors
  created_at  timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
);

-- HNSW: graph-based, high recall, high memory, slow build
CREATE INDEX doc_chunk_hnsw ON doc_chunk
  USING hnsw (embedding vector_cosine_ops)
  WITH (m = 16, ef_construction = 64);

-- IVFFlat: inverted file, fast build, lower recall, needs a representative dataset
CREATE INDEX doc_chunk_ivf ON doc_chunk
  USING ivfflat (embedding vector_cosine_ops)
  WITH (lists = 1000);

SET hnsw.ef_search = 100;                 -- runtime recall/latency dial (session or per-role)
SET ivfflat.probes = 50;                  -- runtime dial for IVF
```

```sql
-- filtered search: the filter runs *after* the index scan, so selectivity decides your recall
SELECT d.id, d.doc_id, d.content,
       1 - (d.embedding <=> $1::vector) AS cosine_similarity
FROM doc_chunk d
WHERE d.tenant_id = $2                      -- hard filter, must be applied
  AND d.created_at > now() - interval '1 year'
ORDER BY d.embedding <=> $1::vector
LIMIT 20;
```

**HNSW vs IVFFlat:**

| Dimension | HNSW | IVFFlat |
|---|---|---|
| Structure | Multi-layer proximity graph | Voronoi cells, then scan a few |
| Recall at equal latency | Higher | Lower |
| Build time | Slow (graph construction), grows superlinearly | Fast |
| Memory | Higher (the graph) | Lower |
| Training data | None needed | Needs a representative sample to train the centroids, so build *after* the data is loaded, and rebuild when the distribution shifts |
| Build on an empty/small table | Poor (a graph over 1k rows is useless) | Terrible (no good centroids) |
| Deletions | Supported; the graph degrades and needs a reindex/vacuum | Supported; deleted vectors degrade the cells |
| Tunable at query time | `hnsw.ef_search` (default 40), `hnsw.iterative_scan` for better filtered recall | `ivfflat.probes` |
| Good at | Tens of thousands to low millions of vectors, read-heavy | Large ingest, batch build, memory-constrained servers |

**Distance metrics:** `<->` is L2 (squared Euclidean), `<=>` is cosine *distance* (so similarity is `1 - distance`; if you normalised your vectors, L2 and cosine ranking agree), and `<#>` is negative inner product (so a larger value is more similar; that is what "max inner product search" means). Pick cosine for text embeddings, L2 for image features, and IP only when you know the model was trained for it. **Store the metric in the operator class name** (`vector_cosine_ops`, `vector_l2_ops`, `vector_ip_ops`) — the index must match the query operator or it is silently unused.

**The filtering problem, stated precisely:** an HNSW search returns its top-k by distance *globally*, then the WHERE clause is applied, so a query with a selective filter can return far fewer than k rows even though thousands of matching rows exist nearby in the space. The fixes, in order of preference: (1) raise `hnsw.ef_search` and enable `hnsw.iterative_scan = strict_order` so the scan continues until enough filtered rows are found; (2) over-fetch (`LIMIT k*20`) and re-filter in SQL; (3) partition the table by tenant and keep a *separate* index per partition so the search space is pre-filtered; (4) for a small, stable candidate set (per-user items, per-tenant documents), build a partial index per tenant where that matters; (5) move to an engine whose ANN supports pre-filtering natively.

**Recall measurement is not optional.** Before you ship, compute ground truth with exact KNN (`ORDER BY embedding <=> $1 LIMIT k` with the index disabled or a sequential scan on a sample), then measure what fraction of those appear in the ANN top-k. Report recall@10 to a stated target (0.95+ for user-facing retrieval is a reasonable bar) and a latency budget. Without this, "we set ef_search to 40 and it looks fine" is not engineering.

**Other pgvector operations that come up:** `halfvec` (float16) and `bit` (binary) for memory, `sparsevec` for BM25-ish sparse vectors, and a scalar-quantization-plus-binary path. The general technique is **quantize then rerank**: retrieve candidates cheaply, then rescore with the full-precision vector (or with a cross-encoder) — a big recall win for a modest latency cost.

**When pgvector is enough:** up to a few million vectors, tens of queries per second, embeddings that must be joined to relational data with the same transaction and the same permissions, a team that already runs Postgres, and a need for hybrid search built from `tsvector` plus a `rrf` combination. **When you graduate:** tens of millions to billions of vectors, thousands of QPS, need for pre-filtered ANN with high recall, multi-region replicas with snapshots, native hybrid (dense + sparse) ranking, or a requirement to never run an index build on the OLTP primary. Hosted and self-managed options exist for both (the pgvector-compatible managed category, plus dedicated engines); name the *category* and the trade-off rather than reciting a leaderboard.

**The non-search half of RAG that interviewers ask about:** chunking strategy (by heading, by token window with overlap, by semantic block) determines recall more than the index does; the embedding model version must be pinned per row (I have a `model` column above for exactly this) because mixing models in one index produces nonsense neighbours; the retrieved context needs a rerank stage; and evaluation needs a labelled set with recall@k, MRR, and answer faithfulness, not vibes.

**Questions**
1. What is a hypertable, and what does it buy you over a partitioned table?
2. What are space partitioning and why would you add it?
3. What are compression settings, and what do `segmentby` and `orderby` control?
4. What is a continuous aggregate, and what is the incremental part?
5. Explain `start_offset` and `end_offset`, and the late-data failure mode.
6. What is `time_bucket_gapfill` for?
7. What breaks when you join a hypertable to a normal table?
8. When is a columnar OLAP engine better than TimescaleDB?
9. What is a Prometheus series, and how does cardinality blow up?
10. Name five label values that must never be labels.
11. What is the head block, and how does it relate to memory?
12. What do recording rules and downsampling solve?
13. What is remote write, and what are its two guarantees?
14. Compare tags and fields in a line protocol.
15. How does HNSW work, at a level you could whiteboard?
16. HNSW vs IVFFlat across recall, build, memory, and tuning.
17. What do the pgvector distance operators mean, and why does the operator class matter?
18. Why does filtered vector search lose recall, and what are the fixes?
19. What is `ef_search` and what happens when you raise it?
20. When do you build an IVFFlat index relative to loading data, and why?
21. How do you measure whether your ANN index is any good?
22. What is quantize-then-rerank?
23. When is pgvector enough, and when do you graduate to a dedicated vector store?
24. Beyond the index, what determines RAG quality?

**Model answer, Q18:** "The problem is that the index and the filter are independent. HNSW walks the proximity graph and returns the globally nearest neighbours, and the WHERE clause is applied afterwards by the executor, so if the filter is selective — say this tenant, or documents from the last year — the top hundred neighbours can contain only two rows that match, and you return two results while thousands of matching rows sit just outside the top hundred. The fixes trade recall, memory, and complexity. First, raise `ef_search` and enable `hnsw.iterative_scan`, which continues the graph walk past the k-th candidate until it has satisfied the filter, trading latency for recall and keeping the strict order. Second, over-fetch: ask for k times twenty and re-filter in SQL, which is simple and works well when the filter is a small fraction of the table. Third, and this is what I would do for a multi-tenant system, partition the table by tenant and keep a separate index per partition so the search space is already filtered — the query only ever searches the tenant's rows, so there is nothing to filter out. Fourth, for a small, stable candidate set like one user's library, a per-tenant or per-user partial index is viable. And if the filter is unpredictable and high-selectivity, that is a signal the engine should be one that supports pre-filtering natively, which is one of the reasons to graduate off pgvector. The key insight for the interview is that recall must be measured against exact KNN ground truth on a labelled set, at the filter selectivities you actually have, not on an unfiltered query where everything looks great."

**Mistakes:** leading a compound index with a range predicate; assuming a shard-key-missing query is a small slowdown; treating `w: 1` as durable; putting a high-cardinality value in a Prometheus label; building a continuous aggregate over "now"; joining a hypertable without a time predicate; using a L2 distance against a cosine-trained model; shipping an ANN index with no recall measurement; expecting a WHERE clause to be free in vector search; mixing embedding models in one index.

**Mini task:** load 2M 1536-dim vectors into Postgres, build HNSW, and chart recall@10 and p99 latency as you sweep `ef_search` from 10 to 500; repeat with a tenant filter at 1%, 10%, and 50% selectivity and watch recall collapse without `iterative_scan`. Then build an IVFFlat index on 50k rows and on 2M rows and compare build time. Separately, run a Prometheus-like load test with a `user_id` label added and watch the series count, then read the TSDB docs on head chunks and explain what would break in production.

---

# 6.24 ORMs and Data Access
Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition:** The layer between your application code and the database: object mappers with a schema DSL and a client, plus query builders, plus the raw-SQL escape hatches. Judging an ORM well is a sign of production experience, because the interesting differences are all in the failure modes.

**Framing for the interview:** an ORM is a *compilation* strategy for SQL. It generates SQL you could have written, which means it is at its best for CRUD and joins, and it is a tax everywhere else. The mature position is: use the ORM for the schema, migrations, and the boring 80%; drop to SQL for the interesting 20%; and never let the ORM hide a query you cannot read.

## 6.24.1 Prisma

```prisma
// schema.prisma
datasource db {
  provider = "postgresql"
  url      = env("DATABASE_URL")
}

generator client {
  provider = "prisma-client-js"
}

enum OrderStatus { PENDING PAID SHIPPED CANCELLED }

model Customer {
  id        String   @id @default(cuid())
  email     String   @unique
  name      String?
  createdAt DateTime @default(now()) @db.Timestamptz(6)
  orders    Order[]
  @@index([createdAt(sort: Desc)])
  @@map("customers")
}

model Order {
  id          String      @id @default(cuid())
  customerId  String
  customer    Customer    @relation(fields: [customerId], references: [id], onDelete: Restrict)
  status      OrderStatus @default(PENDING)
  totalCents  Int
  currency    String      @default("EUR") @db.VarChar(3)
  placedAt    DateTime    @default(now()) @db.Timestamptz(6)
  items       OrderItem[]

  // the query-shape index: equality prefix + sort. See 6.1-6.17 for the ESR rule.
  @@index([customerId, placedAt(sort: Desc)])
  @@index([status, placedAt(sort: Desc)])
  @@map("orders")
}

model OrderItem {
  id             String @id @default(cuid())
  orderId        String
  order          Order  @relation(fields: [orderId], references: [id], onDelete: Cascade)
  sku            String
  qty            Int
  unitPriceCents Int
  @@unique([orderId, sku])                 // a real DB constraint, not app code
  @@index([sku])
  @@map("order_items")
}
```

```ts
// One client per process. Never per request.
import { PrismaClient } from '@prisma/client';
const globalForPrisma = globalThis as unknown as { prisma?: PrismaClient };
export const prisma = globalForPrisma.prisma ?? new PrismaClient({
  log: ['warn', 'error'],
});
```
