```ts
if (process.env.NODE_ENV !== 'production') globalForPrisma.prisma = prisma;
```
**Why the singleton:** Prisma (and every pooled client) holds a connection pool. Creating a client per request opens a new pool per request, and Postgres has a hard `max_connections`; the classic symptom is "too many clients already" in development and a connection storm in production. In serverless, one client per warm invocation is the pattern, or a pooled proxy in front (PgBouncer, a Data Proxy, or Prisma's Accelerate). The `globalThis` cache is only for dev hot-reload.

| Capability | How | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Migrations | `prisma migrate dev` (dev: creates, applies, and can **reset** the database when it detects drift), `prisma migrate deploy` (prod: applies pending migrations only, never resets) | Never run `migrate dev` in production. It is designed to throw data away |
| Prototype shortcut | `prisma db push` | Syncs the schema without creating migration files. Prototyping and throwaway environments only — no history, no rollback, and no record of what changed |
| Partial selects | `select: { id: true, name: true }` | First class and pushed into the SQL `SELECT` list. One of Prisma's real strengths |
| Relations | `include`, `select` nested | On Postgres with `relationLoadStrategy: "join"` this becomes a SQL join; without it, or on MongoDB, it is a **second query per relation**, which is the N+1 |
| Interactive transaction | `prisma.$transaction(async (tx) => { ... }, { timeout: 5000, maxWait: 2000 })` | `tx` is bound to one connection. **Every call must use `tx`, never the global `prisma`** |
| Batch transaction | `prisma.$transaction([q1, q2, q3])` | Wrapped in one transaction, no callback, so it can be sent as one round trip |
| Raw SQL | `prisma.$queryRaw` (typed returns), `prisma.$executeRaw`, `prisma.$queryRawUnsafe` (no parameterisation — injection risk) | Use tagged templates: `prisma.$queryRaw\`SELECT ... WHERE id = ${id}\`` |
| Middleware | `prisma.$use(async (params, next) => { ... })` | Cross-cutting concerns (soft delete scoping, tenant filtering, logging). Fast, but it is a hidden global behaviour that surprises the next reader |
| Upsert | `upsert` maps to `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT` | A real single-statement upsert, which is what you want for idempotency |
| `createMany` | Batch insert, no `returning` on all versions | Much faster than a loop of `create` |
| Connection pooling | `connection_limit` in the URL, `pgbouncer` in the URL | Set it; the default of no limit is how you exhaust `max_connections` |

**Honest limits:**
- **No partial-column *fetching* problem, but no partial-*expression* support either**: you cannot ask for `COUNT(*)`, a `GROUP BY`, a `HAVING`, a window function, a `LATERAL` join, a CTE, a `DISTINCT ON`, or a recursive CTE in the query API. All of it is `$queryRaw`, which means no compile-time verification of the result shape.
- **DDL control is incomplete.** Extensions, row-level security policies, triggers, functions, materialized views, custom domains, `EXCLUDE` constraints, and most Postgres-specific types are outside the migration engine. The escape hatch is a hand-written SQL migration file added to the migrations folder, which works and which nobody likes, but it is the honest answer.
- **No stored procedures or database functions** in the query API.
- `updateMany` and `deleteMany` return counts, not the affected rows, so a "soft delete then read the rows" pattern needs a second query or a raw statement.
- The generated client is large and the query engine is a binary, which matters for edge runtimes and cold starts.
- The schema is the source of truth, which means a database change without a schema change is drift, and drift is the single most common source of "works on my machine".

## 6.24.2 Drizzle

```ts
import { pgTable, pgEnum, text, integer, timestamp, index, uniqueIndex,
         primaryKey, check, sql } from 'drizzle-orm/pg-core';
import { drizzle } from 'drizzle-orm/node-postgres';
import { relations } from 'drizzle-orm';

export const orderStatus = pgEnum('order_status', ['pending', 'paid', 'shipped', 'cancelled']);

export const customers = pgTable('customers', {
  id:        text('id').primaryKey(),
  email:     text('email').notNull().unique(),
  name:      text('name'),
  createdAt: timestamp('created_at', { withTimezone: true }).defaultNow().notNull(),
}, (t) => ({
  createdIdx: index('customers_created_idx').on(t.createdAt.desc()),
}));

export const orders = pgTable('orders', {
  id:         text('id').primaryKey(),
  customerId: text('customer_id').notNull().references(() => customers.id, { onDelete: 'restrict' }),
  status:     orderStatus('status').notNull().default('pending'),
  totalCents: integer('total_cents').notNull().check(sql`total_cents >= 0`),
  placedAt:   timestamp('placed_at', { withTimezone: true }).defaultNow().notNull(),
}, (t) => ({
  // the query-shape index
  customerPlacedIdx: index('orders_customer_placed_idx').on(t.customerId, t.placedAt.desc()),
  statusPlacedIdx:   index('orders_status_placed_idx').on(t.status, t.placedAt.desc()),
}));

// relations() powers the relational query API: db.query.orders.findMany({ with: { customer: true } })
export const ordersRelations = relations(orders, ({ one, many }) => ({
  customer: one(customers, { fields: [orders.customerId], references: [customers.id] }),
  items:    many(orderItems),
}));
```

```ts
const db = drizzle(pool);

// typed, composable, and you can read the SQL it produces
const rows = await db
  .select({ id: orders.id, total: orders.totalCents })
  .from(orders)
  .where(and(eq(orders.status, 'paid'), gte(orders.placedAt, lastWeek)))
  .orderBy(desc(orders.placedAt))
  .limit(50);

// a hand-written fragment inside a query builder
const top = await db
  .select({ sku: orderItems.sku, revenue: sql<number>`sum(${orderItems.qty} * ${orderItems.unitPriceCents})` })
  .from(orderItems)
  .groupBy(orderItems.sku)
  .having(sql`sum(${orderItems.qty}) > ${10}`)
  .orderBy(desc(sql`sum(${orderItems.qty} * ${orderItems.unitPriceCents})`));

// relational API, a real SQL join under the hood
const withCustomer = await db.query.orders.findMany({
  where: eq(orders.status, 'shipped'),
  with: { customer: { columns: { id: true, name: true } } },
  limit: 20,
});
```

- **Drizzle is SQL-first:** the schema *is* the SQL table definition, and the query builder is a typed wrapper over SQL, so anything SQL can express, Drizzle can express (usually with a `sql` template escape hatch). That is the opposite trade from Prisma's higher-level, safer-but-more-opinionated query API.
- **It is thin**, so there is no query compiler, no engine, no shadow database, and a much smaller runtime — which matters for serverless cold starts and edge runtimes.
- **Migrations** are generated as SQL files with a `meta/_journal.json`, and `drizzle-kit push` is the prototyping shortcut. Because migrations are just SQL, you can hand-edit them freely, which is a real advantage when you need an exotic DDL statement.
- **It has real Postgres surface:** `pgPolicy` for row-level security, `pgRole`, `check`, `foreignKey`, `uniqueIndex`, and composite `primaryKey`. What it does *not* have first-class support for is `EXCLUDE` constraints and some extension types, so those go in a hand-written migration.
- **Trade-off versus Prisma:** Drizzle gives you SQL reviewability and no abstraction tax, and gives up the generated-client type safety across an entire data layer plus the nicest migrate workflow. For a team that reads SQL comfortably and wants the database to stay the source of truth, Drizzle is often the better fit; for a large team that wants uniform, safe, generated access, Prisma is.

## 6.24.3 TypeORM and Sequelize: the decorator generations

**TypeORM:**
```ts
@Entity('orders')
export class Order {
  @PrimaryGeneratedColumn('uuid') id!: string;
  @Column({ type: 'enum', enum: OrderStatus, default: 'pending' }) status!: OrderStatus;
  @Column({ name: 'total_cents', type: 'int' }) totalCents!: number;
  @Column({ name: 'placed_at', type: 'timestamptz' }) placedAt!: Date;

  @ManyToOne(() => Customer, c => c.orders, { onDelete: 'RESTRICT' })
  @JoinColumn({ name: 'customer_id' }) customer!: Customer;

  @OneToMany(() => OrderItem, i => i.order) items!: OrderItem[];
}
// repository style
const orders = dataSource.getRepository(Order);
const shipped = await orders.find({ where: { status: 'shipped' }, relations: { customer: true }, take: 20 });
```
- **Strengths:** decorators give you a rich metadata model, it works across Postgres/MySQL/SQLite/Mongo, `DataSource` is a real configuration object, `@AfterLoad`/`@BeforeInsert` hooks are convenient, and the `QueryBuilder` is a complete escape hatch.
- **Drawbacks:** the decorator + `emitDecoratorMetadata` setup is a compiler-configuration dependency; the entity metadata graph is hydrated at bootstrap (a real cost with hundreds of entities and a notorious source of slow test start-up); `relations: { customer: true }` in a loop is N+1; lazy relations were historically `Promise<T>` in some versions which changes typing everywhere; `synchronize: true` (schema auto-sync) will happily drop columns and must never be used against anything you care about; and the generated SQL is harder to predict than Drizzle's or Prisma's.
- **When to choose it:** an existing codebase, a team already fluent in it, or a multi-dialect requirement that justifies the abstraction.

**Sequelize:**
```ts
class Order extends Model {}
Order.init({
  id:         { type: DataTypes.UUID, primaryKey: true, defaultValue: DataTypes.UUIDV4 },
  status:     { type: DataTypes.ENUM(...), defaultValue: 'pending' },
  totalCents: { type: DataTypes.INTEGER, allowNull: false, validate: { min: 0 } },
  placedAt:   { type: DataTypes.DATE, defaultValue: DataTypes.NOW },
}, { modelName: 'order', tableName: 'orders', paranoid: true, underscored: true });

await Order.bulkCreate(rows);                       // one statement, hooks fire per row
await Order.create(data, { transaction: tx });
await Order.destroy({ where: { id }, transaction: tx });   // paranoid: soft delete
await Order.update({ status: 'shipped' }, { where: { id }, transaction: tx });

// N+1 trap: one query, then one per order
const orders = await Order.findAll({ include: [{ model: Customer, as: 'customer' }] });
// fix: let the DB do the join with a separate (non-joined) eager load
const batched = await Order.findAll({
  include: [{ model: Customer, as: 'customer', separate: true }],
});
// or: raw SQL
const rows = await sequelize.query(`
  SELECT o.*, c.email AS "customer.email"
  FROM orders o JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
  WHERE o.status = $1 ORDER BY o.placed_at DESC LIMIT $2`,
  { type: QueryTypes.SELECT, bind: ['shipped', 20], mapToModel: false });
```
- **Strengths:** mature, `paranoid` soft deletes, model and association hooks that are genuinely useful, and a well-known escape hatch to raw SQL.
- **Drawbacks:** association `include` silently produces N+1 unless you use `separate: true`; `Op` symbol comparisons are verbose; `group` + `attributes: [[fn('COUNT', col('id')), 'count']]` is clunky; `sync({ alter: true })` is destructive; and the general experience is "I am fighting the ORM" more often than with Prisma or Drizzle.
- **Hooks** (`beforeBulkCreate`, `afterCreate`, `afterFind`) are the feature people miss: they are the natural place for audit stamping, but they fire per row on `bulkCreate`, so an N+1 log write is one keystroke away.

## 6.24.4 Mongoose

```ts
const orderSchema = new Schema({
  customerId: { type: ObjectId, ref: 'Customer', required: true, index: true },
  status:      { type: String, enum: ['pending', 'paid', 'shipped'], default: 'pending', index: true },
  totalCents:  { type: Number, required: true, min: 0 },
  placedAt:    { type: Date, default: Date.now },
  items: [{
    sku: String, qty: { type: Number, required: true }, unitPriceCents: { type: Number, required: true },
  }],
}, { timestamps: true, optimisticConcurrency: true });

orderSchema.index({ customerId: 1, placedAt: -1 });          // equality prefix + sort (see 6.19.3)

// documents vs plain objects
const docs  = await Order.find(filter);            // hydrated Mongoose documents
const plain = await Order.find(filter).lean();     // POJOs: no getters, no middleware, much faster

// populate = a client-side join; .select controls which fields come back
const withCustomer = await Order.find({ status: 'shipped' })
  .select('+totals')                               // schema `select: false` fields
  .populate({ path: 'customer', select: 'name email' })
  .sort({ placedAt: -1 })
  .limit(20)
  .lean();

// virtuals
orderSchema.virtual('itemCount').get(function (this: OrderDoc) { return this.items.length; });
orderSchema.virtual('customer').get(function () {          // computed ref, resolved by populate
  return this.$populated('customer') ?? undefined;
});
orderSchema.set('toJSON', { virtuals: true });

// middleware: note that query middleware does NOT fire for updateMany/deleteMany
orderSchema.pre('save', function (next) { this.updatedBy = currentUserId; next(); });
orderSchema.pre('find', function () { if (!this.getQuery().includeArchived) this.where({ archivedAt: null }); });
```
- **`lean()` is the single biggest Mongoose performance lever.** It skips document hydration, getters, virtuals, and change tracking. Use it for read-heavy paths and accept that you lose methods and middleware; validate at the boundary with a schema (Zod) instead.
- **`populate` is a second query per path** and is the classic Mongoose N+1. `populate` on a large array is worse: it fetches by `$in` over the whole array, so a document with 10,000 items produces a 10,000-element `$in`.
- **`updateMany` and `deleteMany` bypass document middleware**; you need `pre('updateMany')` / `pre('deleteMany')` explicitly. This is a genuine surprise: soft-delete hooks that "always run" do not.
- **Discriminated unions** map cleanly onto Mongo's discriminator pattern, which is one of the better arguments for a document store.
- `strictQuery`, `autoIndex: false` in production (you create indexes from migrations, not at boot), and `ObjectId` casting to strings (JSON serialisation turns an ObjectId into a hex string, so a `Types.ObjectId` type on the client is a lie unless you transform it).

## 6.24.5 The N+1 problem and its four fixes

```ts
// The disease: 1 + N queries. 500 orders -> 501 round trips, ~250ms of pure latency.
const orders = await db.order.findMany({ take: 500 });
for (const o of orders) {
  o.customer = await db.customer.findUnique({ where: { id: o.customerId } });
  o.itemCount = await db.orderItem.count({ where: { orderId: o.id } });
}
```

**Fix 1 — DataLoader (batching).** Coalesce all the identifiers requested in one tick into a single query and share the result per request.
```ts
const userById = new DataLoader<string, User>(async (ids) => {
  const rows = await prisma.user.findMany({ where: { id: { in: [...ids] } } });
  const map = new Map(rows.map(r => [r.id, r]));
  return ids.map(id => map.get(id) ?? null);        // order and length MUST match
});

// In a request-scoped container, so caching is per request and not global:
const loaders = { userById, orderItemCount: new DataLoader<string, number>(...) };
```
**Fix 2 — eager loading as a join.** `include` with `relationLoadStrategy: "join"` (Prisma on Postgres), Drizzle's relational API, a TypeORM `relations` with a join strategy, or a `leftJoin` in a query builder. One query, no batching logic. The cost is a wider result set and hydration work; the benefit is no second round trip.

**Fix 3 — split queries.** Fetch the parents, collect the distinct foreign keys, fetch the children with `WHERE id IN (...)` or `IN` on the child column, and stitch in memory. This is what `separate: true` does in Sequelize and what `relationLoadStrategy: "query"` does in Prisma. Same round-trip count as batching, no per-request cache, but no loader plumbing either — often the best answer for a read-only endpoint.

**Fix 4 — DataLoader over an existing cache.** A DataLoader instance cached on the request context also gives you request-level memoization, so a component that asks for the same user five times does one query. The bug to avoid is a **module-level** DataLoader, which caches forever and serves one user's data to the next user. That is a data-leak bug, and it is worth naming unprompted.

```ts
// Drizzle: split query, in about five lines
const orders = await db.query.orders.findMany({ take: 500, with: { items: true } });
const ids = orders.map(o => o.customerId);
const customers = await db.select().from(customers).where(inArray(customers.id, ids));
const byId = new Map(customers.map(c => [c.id, c]));
const result = orders.map(o => ({ ...o, customer: byId.get(o.customerId) }));
```

**How to detect it:** query counts in tests, APM spans grouped by statement shape (`findUnique` called 500 times is a screaming histogram), Postgres `pg_stat_statements` showing a cheap statement executed thousands of times per second, and a p99 that scales linearly with page size.

## 6.24.6 Raw SQL versus an ORM

| Dimension | ORM / query builder | Raw SQL |
|---|---|---|
| Type safety of results | Generated or inferred | None (you type the return, which drifts silently) |
| Injection risk | Parameterised by construction | Only with tagged templates or real bind parameters; string interpolation is a vulnerability |
| Expressiveness | CRUD, joins, simple aggregates | Everything: `GROUP BY`, `HAVING`, windows, CTEs, `DISTINCT ON`, `LATERAL`, `EXPLAIN`, `FOR UPDATE`, `DISTINCT` tricks, hints |
| Readability of the generated SQL | You have to inspect it | It is what you wrote |
| Refactoring safety | Schema change propagates | You own every string |
| Portability | Some portability (VARCHAR vs TEXT, `RETURNING`, upsert syntax) | None; you are now Postgres-only |
| Startup and cold start | Prisma ships an engine; Drizzle does not | Zero |
| Debugging | A layer between you and the error | Direct |

**The position to give:** "The ORM is the compiler for the 80% of queries that are CRUD-shaped. For the 20% that are reporting, recursive, or performance-sensitive, I write the SQL, and I keep the schema and migrations in the ORM so there is still one source of truth. I never build a user-facing string by concatenating a sort column — that is both an injection vector and a foot-gun; I whitelist the column, then interpolate the whitelisted identifier."

```ts
// The safe pattern for a dynamic sort/filter
const SORTABLE = { newest: 'placed_at', oldest: 'placed_at', total: 'total_cents' } as const;
type SortKey = keyof typeof SORTABLE;

async function listOrders(opts: { status?: string; sort?: SortKey; limit: number }) {
  const sortCol = SORTABLE[opts.sort ?? 'newest'];                 // whitelisted, never user text
  const dir = opts.sort === 'oldest' ? 'ASC' : 'DESC';            // also from a fixed set
  return db.$queryRawUnsafe<OrderRow[]>`
    SELECT * FROM orders
    WHERE ($1::text IS NULL OR status = $1)
    ORDER BY ${sql.identifier(sortCol)} ${sql.raw(dir)}
    LIMIT ${opts.limit}`;
}
```
(Prisma's `$queryRawUnsafe` is a blunt instrument; Prisma's `$queryRaw` tagged template is the safe form, and Drizzle's `sql.identifier` is the right helper. The point is the *shape*: whitelist the identifier, parameterise the values.)

## 6.24.7 Transactions in ORMs, and the `Promise.all` pitfall

```ts
// Correct: everything inside the callback uses `tx`, which is pinned to ONE connection.
await prisma.$transaction(async (tx) => {
  const a = await tx.account.update({
    where: { id: from, balanceCents: { gte: amount } },        // the guard
    data: { balanceCents: { decrement: amount } },
  });
  await tx.ledger.create({ data: { accountId: from, amountCents: -amount, idempotencyKey } });
  await tx.account.update({ where: { id: to }, data: { balanceCents: { increment: amount } } });
  await tx.ledger.create({ data: { accountId: to, amountCents: amount, idempotencyKey } });
}, { isolationLevel: 'Serializable', timeout: 5000, maxWait: 2000 });
```

**The pitfall, stated precisely:** an interactive transaction checks out a single connection and pins every query on it. If you accidentally reference the global client inside the callback, those queries run on a *different* connection, outside the transaction, and you get partial commits that no test on a small dataset will catch.
```ts
// BROKEN: the global `prisma` escapes the transaction
await prisma.$transaction(async (tx) => {
  await tx.account.update({ ... });
  await prisma.auditLog.create({ ... });     // <-- not in the transaction at all
});
```
**And the `Promise.all` variant.** Within `tx` every query is on one connection, so `Promise.all` gives you no parallelism — the driver queues them and runs them one after another, which is harmless but not a speed-up. Meanwhile, the *default* transaction timeout is 5 seconds with a 2-second `maxWait` for acquiring the connection; an interactive transaction that does a network call inside it will time out, and a batch transaction that runs 200 queries may exceed the database's statement or connection limits. Three rules: no network I/O inside a transaction, no user interaction inside a transaction, and batch `Promise.all` **outside** interactive transactions (where each query gets its own connection) or use the batch form `prisma.$transaction([q1, q2, q3])`.

**Retry on serialization failure.** `SERIALIZABLE` and `REPEATABLE READ` (Postgres) fail with a serialization error under contention, and the caller must retry with backoff and jitter. Prisma surfaces this as `P2034`; with `node-postgres` you catch SQLSTATE `40001` and retry. Retry a bounded number of times (3) with exponential backoff plus jitter, and make every statement in the transaction idempotent so a retry cannot double-apply. Cross-reference 6.1–6.17 for isolation levels, explicit locking, deadlock avoidance, and lock-ordering discipline.

**Lock ordering to avoid deadlocks:** when a transaction touches several rows, always lock them in a deterministic order — sort the ids ascending, then `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` in that order. `ORDER BY id FOR UPDATE` is the single most valuable habit in a high-contention service.

## 6.24.8 Migrations that do not take the site down

**Naming and mechanics.** `20260101120000_add_orders_status_idx` — timestamp prefix so ordering is lexical and correct, then a verb, then the object. A `migrations` table (Prisma `_prisma_migrations`, Drizzle `__drizzle_migrations`, Flyway's `schema_version`) is the source of truth for what has been applied; `migrate status` in CI must be clean or the deploy is blocked.

**The expand/contract pattern (zero-downtime, additive-only migrations):**

| Step | Change | Compatible with old code? | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1. Expand | Add the new nullable column, or the new table | Yes | No rewrite, no lock of consequence. Deploy code that writes both and reads new-or-old |
| 2. Backfill | Copy old rows to the new shape in batches | Yes | `UPDATE ... WHERE id > $lastId ORDER BY id LIMIT 5000` in a loop, rate-limited, resumable, with a progress checkpoint |
| 3. Switch reads | Deploy code that reads the new column | Yes (both are populated) | Verify parity: compare counts and checksums old vs new |
| 4. Enforce | Add the constraint or `NOT NULL` (see lock notes below) | Yes | Only after the backfill is verified complete |
| 5. Contract | Drop the old column in a **later** release | Yes | Never in the same deploy as step 1 |

**Why each step is separated:** the old code and the new code must both work at all times, because during a rolling deploy and for the duration of a rollback, both versions are running. A migration that renames a column and a deploy that assumes the new name are guaranteed to break one of them.

**Lock risks, concretely (Postgres):**

| Operation | Lock | Rewrite? | Safe pattern |
|---|---|---|---|
| `ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN x text` | Brief `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` | No | Fine, but set `lock_timeout` so it queues rather than blocks everything behind it |
| `ADD COLUMN x int NOT NULL DEFAULT 5` | Brief | No since PG 11 (a missing-value fast path) | Fine on PG 11+; a **rewrite** on PG 10 and earlier |
| `ADD COLUMN x int DEFAULT nextval('s')` | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` **for the whole table** | No (volatile default) | Every concurrent `SELECT` blocks. Add the column, then `SET DEFAULT` in a separate statement |
| `CREATE INDEX` | `SHARE`, blocks writes | No | `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` (cannot run inside a transaction, can fail leaving an invalid index — check and retry) |
| `CREATE INDEX ... WHERE partial` | as above | No | Same, `CONCURRENTLY` |
| `ALTER COLUMN TYPE` | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` | **Yes**, for most type changes | Add a new column, backfill, swap. Or use a binary-coercible type change (varchar(50) -> varchar(100)) which does not rewrite |
| `SET NOT NULL` | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE`, plus a full scan to verify | Scan only | PG 12+: add `CHECK (x IS NOT NULL) NOT VALID`, `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` (which takes `SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE`, not blocking reads or writes), then `SET NOT NULL` (which skips the scan because the validated check proves it) |
| `ADD FOREIGN KEY` | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` on **both** tables | No | `ADD ... NOT VALID`, then `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` |
| `ADD EXCLUDE constraint` (no-overlap) | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` | No, but validates existing rows | `NOT VALID` + `VALIDATE`; make sure you have a matching `btree_gist` index |
| `DROP COLUMN` | `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` (metadata only) | No | Cheap, but do it in a later release |
| Adding a default to a hot table | as above | No | See the volatile-default trap |

**Operational guard rails:** every migration session sets `lock_timeout` (e.g. 3s) and `statement_timeout`, so a blocked migration fails fast and retries on a schedule rather than queueing every query behind it for minutes. Retry migrations on lock errors with backoff. For a very large table where even a metadata-only `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` is too dangerous at peak, use `pt-online-schema-change` or `pg_repack`, or accept a dual-write period. And a migration must be **forward-only in practice**: down migrations are usually untested, and the honest rollback plan is "roll forward with a fix", which is why the expand step must be genuinely reversible by old code.

**Seeding and deterministic factories.**
```ts
// dev-only seed, idempotent, deterministic
const seed = async (url: string) => {
  const db = new PrismaClient({ datasources: { db: { url } } });
  const rnd = mulberry32(1337);                       // seeded PRNG: same data every run
  for (let i = 0; i < 1000; i++) {
    const id = `user_${i.toString().padStart(6, '0')}`;   // deterministic ids
    await db.user.upsert({ where: { id }, create: { id, email: `u${i}@test.dev` }, update: {} });
  }
  await db.$disconnect();
};
```
Rules: seed through `upsert`, never `create`, so a re-run is safe; pin the PRNG seed so a failing test reproduces; use stable ids so foreign keys in other fixtures resolve; never read `Date.now()` in a factory when a fixed timestamp would do; keep seeds out of production paths entirely (a separate command, not a module import); and make the dataset *skewed*, because uniform data hides index and cardinality problems (a few users with 10k orders, 95% with none).

**Drift detection:** the dangerous state is a database whose schema no longer matches the schema definition. Prisma detects it in dev and offers to reset (which is why `migrate dev` is fine in dev and catastrophic in prod); Drizzle has `drizzle-kit check` and a `pull` that introspects; TypeORM has `schema:log`. In CI, run `migrate status` and a diff between the migration history and the expected schema, and fail the build on drift.

## 6.24.9 The repository pattern, honestly

**What it is:** an interface (or abstract class) in the domain layer that the rest of the code depends on, with a database-backed implementation and, usually, an in-memory one for tests.
```ts
export interface OrderRepository {
  save(order: Order): Promise<void>;
  findById(id: OrderId): Promise<Order | null>;
  findByCustomer(customerId: CustomerId, opts: Page<Order>): Promise<Page<Order>>;
  reserveItems(orderId: OrderId, expected: Version): Promise<boolean>;   // optimistic
}

export class PostgresOrderRepository implements OrderRepository { /* ... */ }
export class InMemoryOrderRepository implements OrderRepository { /* ... */ }
```

**When it earns its keep:**
- **Testability without a database.** A unit test for business logic runs in 1ms with an in-memory double instead of 300ms against a container. This is the strongest argument and it is real.
- **The domain stays free of the ORM.** No Prisma types in the domain, so you can change persistence without touching business rules. This matters at the boundary of a large codebase.
- **Multiple backends or migrations in flight** (a strangler-fig migration from one store to another, or a test-time in-memory substitute).
- **A stable, mockable seam for a large team** where "how do I get the user's email" has one answer.
- **A place to put the transaction boundary and the unit of work**, so callers cannot accidentally do half a unit of work.

**When it is pure indirection:**
- One backend, forever, and a team of ten or fewer. You have added a file, a mapping function, and a layer of indirection with no new capability.
- **The mapping tax:** every repository method converts rows to domain objects, and that conversion is code you must write, test, and keep in sync. If the mapping is a rename per field, it is pure cost.
- **It hides power.** The moment you need a window function, a `LATERAL` join, or a lock, the repository interface either grows a leaky method or you reach around it. A repository that has a method per query is a query builder with ceremony.
- **It becomes a "one repository per table" dumping ground,** which is the classic failure. A repository should be shaped by *use cases* and return *aggregates*, not mirror tables row by row.
- **A generic `BaseRepository<T>`** with `findAll`, `findById`, `delete` gives you type safety and zero design.

**The test that settles it:** if you deleted the repository interface and called Prisma directly, would your unit tests become meaningfully worse? If the answer is "no, they would just need a database fixture", keep the interface. If the answer is "yes, they would need a running Postgres", the interface is earning its keep.

**Query builders.** You need one when filters are genuinely dynamic (a list page with a user-selected filter set). Design it so the *shape* is fixed and only *values* vary, and always parameterise. Never build SQL by string concatenation; a sort column, a direction, and a table name must come from a whitelist because an identifier cannot be parameterised. A good builder returns a plan you can print, so tests assert the generated SQL and reviewers can read it.

```ts
type OrderFilter = { status?: OrderStatus; customerId?: string; from?: Date; to?: Date; minTotal?: number };
const ORDER_COLUMNS = { total: 'total_cents', placed: 'placed_at' } as const;

function buildOrderQuery(f: OrderFilter, sort: keyof typeof ORDER_COLUMNS, dir: 'ASC'|'DESC', limit: number) {
  const where: string[] = [];
  const params: unknown[] = [];
  if (f.status)    { params.push(f.status);    where.push(`status = $${params.length}`); }
  if (f.customerId){ params.push(f.customerId);where.push(`customer_id = $${params.length}`); }
  if (f.from)      { params.push(f.from);      where.push(`placed_at >= $${params.length}`); }
  if (f.minTotal)  { params.push(f.minTotal);  where.push(`total_cents >= $${params.length}`); }
  return {
    sql: `SELECT * FROM orders
          ${where.length ? 'WHERE ' + where.join(' AND ') : ''}
          ORDER BY ${ORDER_COLUMNS[sort]} ${dir}
          LIMIT $${params.length + 1}`,
    params: [...params, limit],
  };
}
```
Compare the generated SQL to the index design in 6.1–6.17: a builder that can emit `WHERE status = $1 AND placed_at > $2 ORDER BY placed_at` with no index on `(status, placed_at)` is a builder that will be blamed for a slow query in production.

**Questions**
1. Prisma, Drizzle, TypeORM, Sequelize, Mongoose: which is SQL-first and which is object-first, and what does that change?
2. Why must a Prisma (or any) client be a singleton, and what breaks in serverless?
3. What is the difference between `migrate dev`, `migrate deploy`, and `db push`?
4. What does `relationLoadStrategy: "join"` do, and when does Prisma fall back to a second query?
5. List five things the Prisma query API cannot express.
6. How do you write a dynamic sort safely in Prisma, and why is `sql.identifier` needed?
7. What is Drizzle's `relations()` for, and what does `with` compile to?
8. What does Drizzle's `sql` template escape hatch buy you?
9. Why is `synchronize: true` in TypeORM dangerous?
10. What is Mongoose's `.lean()` and what do you lose?
11. Why does `populate` produce N+1, and what is the fix?
12. Which Mongoose middleware does not fire for `updateMany`, and why does that matter?
13. What are Prisma's `maxWait` and `timeout` on an interactive transaction?
14. Why must every query inside `$transaction(async tx => ...)` use `tx`?
15. What actually happens if you `Promise.all` several queries on `tx`?
16. Write the correct transfer transaction with an idempotency key and a guard.
17. How do you handle a serialization failure (P2034 / SQLSTATE 40001)?
18. What is the four-step expand/contract migration pattern, and why must each step be a separate deploy?
19. Which DDL statements take `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` on Postgres, and which of them rewrite the table?
20. How do you add a `NOT NULL` column to a 500M-row table without downtime?
21. How do you add a foreign key to a large table safely?
22. What is `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY`'s failure mode?
23. Why are down migrations usually a bad idea, and what is the real rollback plan?
24. How do you detect schema drift in CI?
25. Why must seeds be deterministic and idempotent?
26. When does a repository pattern earn its keep, and when is it indirection?
27. What is wrong with a generic `BaseRepository<T>`?
28. What is a query builder for, and what must never be string-concatenated?
29. What is the "test that settles it" for keeping a repository?
30. Name three symptoms of an N+1 in production and how you detect it.

**Model answer, Q14:** "An interactive transaction checks out one connection from the pool and pins the callback to it, so every statement in the transaction runs on that single connection, in order. If you reference the global client inside the callback, that reference resolves to a different connection, the statements run outside the transaction, and you get a partial commit. The transaction commits as if everything succeeded, but the audit row, the ledger entry, or the email was written on another connection and is not part of the atomic unit. Small tests will not catch it, because the statements usually still succeed; it shows up under load, or when one of the outside-the-transaction statements fails, and then the ledger is inconsistent with the balance. Two more traps in the same area: `Promise.all` inside an interactive transaction gives you no parallelism, because the queries queue on one connection, and if you want parallelism you must run them outside the transaction or in separate connections, which costs atomicity; and the default interactive transaction timeout is five seconds with a two-second wait for the connection, so any network call inside the callback times out the whole transaction. My rule is: interactive transactions contain database statements only, and every statement uses the transaction handle."

**Model answer, Q18:** "The pattern is four deploys, and the reason it is four is that during a rolling deploy, and for as long as a rollback might be needed, the old code and the new code are both running. So the migration must be additive and the code must be compatible with both shapes. Step one, expand: add the new column as nullable, or add the new table, and deploy code that writes to both and reads new-or-old, preferring the new value. Step two, backfill: copy existing rows in batches, with a resumable checkpoint, so you are not holding one long transaction on a large table, and rate-limited so you do not steal capacity from live traffic. Step three, switch reads: deploy code that reads only the new column, and verify parity between the two while it runs. Step four, contract: enforce the constraint or `NOT NULL`, and drop the old column in a later release. The two things that go wrong in practice are doing steps one and two in the same deploy, so the first deploy is slow, and dropping the old column too early, so the rollback path is gone. On Postgres specifically I also use `NOT VALID` plus `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` for foreign keys and `NOT NULL`, which is the difference between blocking writes for the length of a scan and not blocking them."

**Mistakes:** creating a client per request; running `migrate dev` in production; `sync({alter: true})` against real data; concatenating a sort column into SQL; a module-level DataLoader that leaks data between users; `Promise.all` inside an interactive transaction expecting parallelism; putting an HTTP call inside a transaction; long interactive transactions holding a pool connection; expanding and contracting in one deploy; `CREATE INDEX` without `CONCURRENTLY` on a hot table; a `BaseRepository<T>` that mirrors tables; forgetting to set `lock_timeout` in migration sessions.

**Mini task:** build the same CRUD service three ways — Prisma, Drizzle, and raw `pg` with hand-written SQL — and compare: lines of code, cold start, type inference quality, the N+1 fix effort, and how long a window function took to write. Then write a migration that adds a `NOT NULL` column with a default to a 10M-row table and prove, with `EXPLAIN` and by watching concurrent writes, that the add is instant and the `SET DEFAULT` step is the one that blocks. Finally, implement a repository interface with a Postgres and an in-memory implementation and measure how much the unit-test suite slows down without it.

---

# 6.25 Search, OLTP vs OLAP, and Object Storage
Advanced · **Should-know**

**Definition:** Full-text search as an inverted index with relevance scoring, the OLTP/OLAP split and the pipelines that feed it, and the semantics of putting bytes in object storage.

## 6.25.1 The inverted index, tokenization, and analyzers

```
             TERM DICTIONARY                  POSTINGS
 term ─────────────────────────────►  doc_id : tf, positions[...]
"red"   ──► [17, 91, 205, 388, ...]     doc 17: red(2) [pos 4, 11], ...
"running" ─► [91, 205, ...]             (sorted doc ids, with term frequency
                                         and optionally term positions)
```
- **Inverted index:** a map from term to a sorted list of documents containing it. Document-at-a-time scoring walks the postings and accumulates; term-at-a-time scoring iterates the dictionary and merges. Position data ("positions" in the posting) is what makes phrase queries and phrase proximity possible, at extra storage cost.
- **The analysis chain** decides what a term *is*, and it is the biggest lever on search quality: character filters (strip HTML, `asciifolding` so "café" matches "cafe", a mapping filter for units or synonyms) → a tokenizer (`standard`, `whitespace`, `ngram`, `pattern`, language-specific ones) → token filters (lowercase, stop-word removal, stemming or lemmatization, `synonym_graph`, `edge_ngram` for autocomplete).
- **The classic surprise:** the index is analyzed one way and the query is analyzed another. If the index uses a `stemmer`, the query must too, or "running" will never match "run". Same for `asciifolding` and synonyms. This is the number-one cause of "the search is broken for this one term", and the fix is usually the `search_analyzer` on the field.
- **`keyword` vs `text`:** `text` is analyzed into terms and is not aggregatable or sortable. `keyword` is stored whole, unanalyzed, and is aggregatable, sortable, and exactly matchable. The standard pattern is a multi-field: `"title": { "type": "text", "fields": { "raw": { "type": "keyword" } } }` so you get full-text on `title` and exact/aggregatable `title.raw`.
- **Synonyms are a deployment event, not a config event.** Changing a synonym file changes the analysis of the whole index, so you either reindex or use a *search-time* synonym set (a `synonym_graph` filter on a `search_analyzer` only), which is why search-time synonyms exist at all. Rule: never put a phrase in a plain `synonym` filter (a `synonym_graph` supports multi-word); keep synonyms in version control with tests; and expect relevance to move when you change them.
- **`edge_ngram`** with `min_gram: 2, max_gram: 15` on a `search_as_you_type`-style subfield gives prefix completion ("re" → "red running"). The cost is index size, and you should cap `max_gram` and only index the subfield, not the original.

## 6.25.2 Relevance: TF-IDF, BM25, boosts, and decay

- **TF-IDF:** score = `tf * idf` where `idf = log(N / df)`. Intuition: a term that appears in nearly every document tells you nothing (common), a rare term tells you a lot. Plain TF explodes on long documents, so it is always normalised.
- **BM25** (the default in Lucene/Elasticsearch/OpenSearch) fixes the normalisation and adds term-frequency saturation:
  ```
  score(D, Q) = Σ  IDF(q) · ( f(q,D) · (k1 + 1) )
                    / ( f(q,D) + k1 · (1 - b + b · |D| / avgdl) )
  ```
  `k1` (default 1.2) controls how quickly repeated terms stop adding value; `b` (default 0.75) controls how much document length is penalised. Set `k1 = 0` for pure boolean behaviour; lower `b` toward 0 if long documents are unfairly penalised, raise it if they deserve to be. Most teams should leave the defaults alone and fix their *analyzers* and *field structure* instead.
- **Field boosts** encode editorial judgement: `title^5`, `tags^3`, `body^1`. Boost the field where the term is more meaningful.
- **Recency decay** for feeds and news: a `function_score` with `gauss` or `exp` on a date field, so a 3-hour-old match beats a 3-week-old match with the same text score. Do this in the query, not by mixing a timestamp into the text.
- **Score combination:** `bool` queries with `must` (participates in scoring and is required), `should` (participates in scoring, optional, and needs `minimum_should_match` when combined with `must`), and **`filter`** (does *not* score, is cacheable, and is the right place for tenant, permission, and status predicates). Putting filters in `filter` clauses rather than `must` is the single biggest relevance-and-latency win in a filtered search: the filter cache can skip the whole clause.

```json
POST /products/_search
{
  "size": 24,
  "query": {
    "function_score": {
      "query": {
        "bool": {
          "must": [
            { "multi_match": {
                "query": "wireless headphones",
                "type": "best_fields",
                "fields": ["title^5", "tags^3", "description^1"],
                "operator": "and"
            }}
          ],
          "filter": [
            { "term": { "tenant_id": "t1" } },
            { "terms": { "status": ["active"] } },
            { "range": { "price": { "lte": 200 } } }
          ]
        }
      },
      "functions": [
        { "gauss": { "created_at": { "origin": "now", "scale": "7d", "offset": "1d", "decay": 0.5 } } },
        { "field_value_factor": { "field": "popularity", "factor": 0.1, "modifier": "log1p" } }
      ],
      "score_mode": "multiply",
      "boost_mode": "multiply"
    }
  },
  "aggs": {
    "brands": { "terms": { "field": "brand.raw", "size": 20 } },
    "price_hist": { "histogram": { "field": "price", "interval": 25 } }
  }
}
```
**Query-type cheat sheet:** `term` (exact, on `keyword`, not analyzed), `match` (analyzed, on `text`), `match_phrase` and `match_phrase_prefix` (ordered adjacency), `prefix`/`wildcard`/`regexp` (expensive; on `keyword` with a rewrite for leading wildcards), `range`, `exists`, `terms` (an `IN`), `nested` (for arrays of objects, needs a `nested` mapping), `has_child`/`has_parent` (a join at query time, expensive), `geo_distance`, and `more_like_this`/`percolate` (usually not what you want).

## 6.25.3 Elasticsearch/OpenSearch internals you must know

- **Shard and replica.** An index is split into primary shards (fixed at creation, so pick the number with growth in mind: `bytes(primary) * shard_count` should fit comfortably in a node, and too many shards means too many small Lucene segments and expensive merges). Each primary has N replicas, which provide redundancy and read capacity. **Writes go to the primary, then are replicated.** Reads go to primaries and replicas by default; you can send a read to a replica with `preference`.
- **The near-real-time model.** This is the single most important operational fact: a write is not searchable until the next **refresh** (default 1s), which creates a new Lucene segment and opens it for search. So `POST /index/_doc` followed immediately by a search can return nothing. Mitigations: `?refresh=wait_for` (wait for the next refresh, used in tests and for read-your-write flows) or `?refresh=true` (force it now, at a cost — never on a write path). A bulk indexer should use the default refresh and accept up to 1s of lag.
- **Refresh interval trade-off:** 1s means near-real-time search and more segments (more merge work, more heap, slower old-segment reads). 30s or `-1` (manual) means a much more efficient, less search-eligible index. Use a long refresh or manual refresh for a reindex-from-scratch or a logs index.
- **`_source` vs doc values vs a stored field:**
  - `_source` is the original JSON, returned by default for every hit. Storing it is required for reindexing, for `update`, and for most debugging. It is the biggest contributor to index size. `_source` filtering (a `source_includes`/`excludes` in the mapping) reduces transfer size, and there is an advanced trick of storing only a diff, but it is not worth the operational cost.
  - **doc values** are a columnar, on-disk, compressed representation of a field, built at index time for numeric, date, keyword, and boolean fields. They power sorting, aggregations, and script fields without touching `_source`. They are not available for `text`.
  - Stored fields (`"store": true`) are the old way to return a field without `_source`; used rarely.
  - A query that filters, sorts, and aggregates can avoid `_source` entirely (`"docvalue_fields"` plus `"_source": false`), which is the right pattern for a huge index where you only need ids and scores.
- **Mapping vs dynamic mapping.** A mapping defines fields and their types. By default Elasticsearch *guesses* types from the first document it sees: `"2026-01-01"` becomes a `date`, `"true"` becomes a `boolean`, a numeric-looking string becomes a `long`. This is a genuine source of outages (a field that is sometimes a number and sometimes a string is a mapping conflict the first time, and then the *entire* indexing request for that shard fails). Controls: `dynamic: "strict"` (reject anything unmapped — best for correctness), `dynamic: "false"` (ignore unknown fields, keep them in `_source`), or `dynamic_templates` (route by pattern: all `*_id` to `keyword`, all `*_at` to `date`).
- **`keyword` vs `text` revisited with a decision rule:** if you will ever sort, aggregate, filter exactly, or join on the field, it must be `keyword`. If you will ever full-text search it, it needs a `text` field. Fields that are *only* used for filtering should be `keyword` (cheap, no analysis). And a keyword field's `ignore_above` (commonly 256) means longer values are indexed as *not indexed* but still stored in `_source` — a silent truncation that breaks aggregations.
- **Mapping explosion.** Every new field name creates a new field in the mapping, which lives in the cluster state, is broadcast to every node, and is capped by `cluster.max_shards_per_node`-adjacent limits (`total_fields.limit`, default 1000). Symptoms: slow cluster-state updates, nodes flapping, and eventually rejected writes. Fixes: `flattened` (one field holding the whole object, indexable but not individually sortable), `object` vs `nested` (`object` flattens and loses per-element association; `nested` keeps it, at the cost of a separate Lucene document per element and slower queries — use `nested` only when you must query array elements independently), dynamic templates, or index-per-entity-type.
- **Aggregations:** `terms` (with `doc_count_error_upper_count` for shard-size skew — always check the error bound before trusting a facet count, and use `composite` for paginating more than 10,000 buckets), `range`/`date_histogram`, `filter`, `nested`, `top_hits` (a sub-search per bucket, powerful and slow), `percentiles` (TDigest, approximate), and `cardinality` (HyperLogLog++, approximate). Every aggregation scans matching docs, so aggregations over high-cardinality `keyword` fields are a memory risk (`search.max_buckets`, `shard_size`).
- **Deep pagination.** `from` + `size` is capped at `index.max_result_window` (10,000) because every skipped result is materialized. Beyond that: `search_after` with a sort tuple, always combined with a `pit` (point in time) so the index cannot change under you mid-pagination. `pit` + `search_after` + a stable sort is the correct "keep scrolling" recipe, and it is also the right answer for polling an index that is being written to continuously.
- **Sorting.** Always sort on a `doc_values`-backed field with a unique tiebreaker (`_shard_doc` when using a PIT, or a monotonic field). A non-deterministic sort produces duplicates and gaps between pages.
- **Index lifecycle management (ILM) / ISM.** A phase model: `hot` (indexed, searchable), `warm` (replicated fewer, still searchable), `cold` (searchable with fewer resources or snapshot only), `frozen` (snapshot, no search), `delete`. Actions: `rollover` on size or age, `shrink` (merge shards to reduce shard count — do this after the index stops being written to), `forcemerge` (fewer, larger segments, faster search, expensive, read-only-ish), `read_only`, `downsample` (roll up metrics), and `delete`. A time-based index (`logs-2026.01.15`) plus `max_age: 1d` is the simplest correct retention story and it beats `deleteByQuery`, which is a full scan.
- **Search-as-you-type:** the `search_as_you_type` field type provides `title`, `title._2gram`, `title._3gram` subfields plus `bool_prefix` and `most_fields` query types, so as the user types you get prefix matching with correct phrase-like behaviour. The alternative is a separate `edge_ngram` field. Both are better than `prefix` queries on `keyword` for latency, because `edge_ngram` is a term lookup in the inverted index while a `prefix` query must walk the term dictionary.
- **Relevance debugging order** (this is a great interview answer): (1) is the document even in the index — check the index, the shard, and the refresh state; (2) is the field mapped as `text` and analyzed the way the query is; (3) is it the right analyzer (language, stemming, synonyms); (4) use `explain: true` or the `_analyze` API on the exact query string to see which clause matched; (5) `match_all` with only the filters, to isolate filter vs query; (6) compare the BM25 components with a `explain` per clause, and check `min_score`; (7) check field boosts and any `function_score` `score_mode`; (8) check that the filter clauses are in `filter` and not silently being scored.

**Before you reach for a search engine:** Postgres full-text search (`tsvector`, `to_tsquery`, GIN, `ts_rank`, and a generated column) handles a few million documents, exact-prefix matching via `pg_trgm`, and filters fine, and it is transactional with your data, which is a genuinely large advantage. Move to Elasticsearch/OpenSearch when you need analyzers, phrase and proximity relevance tuning, faceting at scale, typo tolerance, per-field boosting at scale, or a fuzzy multilingual story, or when the index no longer fits on your primary.

## 6.25.4 OLTP vs OLAP, star schemas, and the pipelines

| Dimension | OLTP | OLAP |
|---|---|---|
| Purpose | Process transactions, one row at a time, low latency | Aggregate, scan millions of rows, high throughput per query |
| Shape | Normalized (3NF), many joins, small rows | Denormalized star/snowflake, wide rows, few joins |
| Access | Point reads and small range scans, by primary key and a few indexes | Full scans of partitions, columnar compression, vectorised execution |
| Schema | Changes often, per release | Changes rarely; a semantic model is a product |
| Concurrency | Many short transactions, row locks, high write rate | Few long queries, no row locks, read-mostly |
| Engine | Postgres, MySQL | Postgres (with care), ClickHouse, BigQuery, Snowflake, Redshift, BigQuery |
| Indexes | B-tree, hash, partial | Columnar segments, min/max skipping, zone maps, bloom filters |

**Star schema rules:**
- One **fact** table at the centre, with the **grain stated in one sentence** ("one row per order line per day"). If you cannot state the grain, you cannot aggregate correctly, and every analyst will aggregate differently.
- **Dimensions** around it (product, customer, date, store), joined by surrogate integer keys. Conformed dimensions shared across facts are what make cross-domain reporting possible.
- **Slowly changing dimensions**: type 1 overwrite (history lost, cheapest), type 2 add a new row with validity dates (correct history, needs `valid_from`/`valid_to` and a unique constraint ensuring no overlapping ranges), type 3 add a separate "previous value" column (rarely the right answer).
- **Snowflake** normalizes dimensions into their own dimension tables to remove redundancy; it reduces storage and query fan-out costs but increases joins. Star denormalizes dimensions to speed up queries. Pick based on how often dimensions are queried independently.
- **Degenerate dimensions** (a fact table with an order number but no order dimension) and **factless fact tables** (a row per attendance, per coverage) are real and worth naming.
- **Partitioning the fact table by date** is the single most valuable physical decision: it enables partition pruning, makes retention a `DROP PARTITION`, and keeps the working set small.
- **Late-arriving facts and restatements** are the hard part of analytics: a fact can change after you have computed aggregates over it. The standard answer is a defined "closed period" policy (today's numbers are provisional, and only closed periods are restated) plus an idempotent re-computation of the affected window. Do not try to make a mutable aggregate incremental over a mutable fact.

**ETL vs ELT, and the three cadences:**

| Cadence | How | Good for | Bad for |
|---|---|---|---|
| Batch | Scheduled job, reads a watermark or a date range, writes a full or delta partition | Nightly warehouse loads, a stable, low-change upstream | Freshness; a broken run means 24h of staleness; a re-run means re-doing a lot |
| Micro-batch | Same as batch, every 1–15 minutes, small windows | Near-real-time dashboards with modest freshness needs | Small-window overhead; many small files; still gap-prone |
| Streaming | Kafka/PubSub consumers, often via CDC from the OLTP database | Low-latency pipelines, many small producers, event-time semantics | Ordering across partitions, exactly-once illusions, operational cost, schema evolution pain |

- **ETL** transforms before loading (clean, dedupe, reshape in a compute layer, then load a small clean set). **ELT** loads raw first, then transforms in the warehouse, where SQL and storage are cheap. ELT has won almost everywhere because raw retention is valuable and the warehouse is the cheapest place to compute.
- **"Exactly once" is an illusion.** Delivery is at-least-once almost everywhere, and end-to-end exactly-once requires a transactional sink, which most sinks are not. What you actually get is **at-least-once delivery plus an idempotent sink**: deduplicate by event id, or use a merge/upsert on a natural key, and make the sink write commutative. This is the answer to give when asked.
- **Late-arriving data** is handled with **event time, not processing time**: use the timestamp inside the event for the window, maintain a **watermark** (the maximum event time seen minus an allowed lateness, advanced periodically), drop or route events older than the watermark to a late-arrival path, and recompute the affected closed windows. If you window on processing time, a five-minute-late event lands in the wrong bucket and your daily totals never reconcile.
- **CDC** (reading the OLTP database's change log — Postgres logical replication / `wal2json` or Debezium, MySQL binlog) gives you a stream with no application changes and full-fidelity deletes and updates. Its costs: schema changes affect consumers, it exposes more data than you might intend (PII, deleted rows), a large transaction becomes one big change event, and slot retention can fill the WAL and stall the primary if a consumer falls behind.
- **Backfills:** re-run a historical range through the same code path, idempotently, into a temporary output or a shadow table, then atomically swap or reconcile. Do not write a special backfill script that behaves differently from the incremental path — that is how the two paths drift. Rate-limit it, checkpoint it, and validate with reconciliation queries (row counts, sums, distinct counts, and a checksum per partition).
- **Data quality and reconciliation, the unglamorous half:** freshness SLAs, uniqueness of the primary key, referential integrity, volume and distribution checks, null-rate monitoring, and a daily reconciliation against the source system of record. A pipeline with no reconciliation is a pipeline that is silently wrong.
- **`dbt`-style modelling discipline:** staging views that rename and type, intermediate models that join, and marts that are presentation-ready. Incremental strategies: `append` (a log; simplest, cheapest, and correct when facts are immutable), `merge`/`upsert` on a unique key (correct for mutable facts and for late arrivals), and `delete+insert` (a partition replace; correct but heavy). Every incremental model must have a declared `unique_key` and a filter predicate, and a full-refresh path that must produce the same result, tested on a schedule.

## 6.25.5 Object storage semantics

- **Multipart upload:** `CreateMultipartUpload` → `UploadPart` (each part at least 5MB except the last, at most 10,000 parts, so the maximum object is about 5TB) → `CompleteMultipartUpload`. Benefits: resumable uploads of large files, parallel throughput, and per-part retry. Costs: you must clean up aborted uploads, and each part is a separate object until completion. **Abandoned multipart uploads are billed forever** — an `AbortIncompleteMultipartUpload` lifecycle rule is the single most valuable line in an S3 lifecycle configuration.
- **ETag semantics:** for a single-part upload the ETag is the MD5 of the content; for a multipart upload it is a hash of the part hashes, so **it is not the MD5** and cannot be used for integrity verification. Use `ChecksumAlgorithm` (SHA-256) per part and per object if you need real verification, and verify on the download path.
- **Presigned URLs:** a signed URL grants time-limited access to a specific key and method, which is how you upload directly from a browser without proxying bytes through your app. Critical details: the expiry (as short as possible, minutes for uploads), the `Content-Type` is part of the signature if you sign it, the URL is a bearer credential (anyone with it has access until expiry), and you must constrain the key prefix per user (`PUT` only under `uploads/<userId>/`) because a client that can choose any key can overwrite anything. Never sign a prefix wildcard.
- **Eventual consistency, carefully:** `PUT`/`GET` of a single object is strongly read-after-write consistent in current S3. What is **eventually** consistent is: `LIST` (directory listings) and `HEAD` for an object that was just deleted or overwritten concurrently, `CopyObject` and `DELETE` interactions, and the replication to a secondary region (which is eventually consistent and is a documented design assumption). For strong consistency on listing behaviour, there is a consistent-read option in newer object stores; if you need a list-and-read invariant, design around it (a manifest object, or a database as the index).
- **No rename and no partial update.** Renaming is `CopyObject` + `Delete`, which for a large object is a full server-side copy (billed) and is not atomic. Any "edit" of an object is a full new object. This is why object storage plus a pointer in a database is the pattern for "immutable objects, mutable references": the object is written once, and the row that points at it is updated in a transaction, and the old version is deleted later.
- **Versioning** gives you rollback, and it interacts badly with cost: every delete becomes a marker, storage grows, and you must add lifecycle rules to expire non-current versions. Versioning is what makes accidental deletion recoverable, and it is worth enabling on anything you cannot regenerate.
- **Lifecycle rules** are the cost control: expire by age, transition to infrequent-access or archive storage classes by age, abort incomplete multipart uploads after N days, and expire previous versions after N days. Archive storage classes have a *minimum* storage duration and a per-request retrieval fee, so a 30-day archive you read every day costs more than you think.
- **Range requests** let you read a byte range, which is how you do partial downloads, parallel multi-part downloads of one object, and efficient range scans over a Parquet or NDJSON dataset. Every serious analytics pipeline on object storage exploits this.
- **Replication** is asynchronous and eventually consistent. Cross-region replication adds latency to the secondary, and a read from the secondary immediately after a write can miss it. Design for that, or accept a single region.
- **The rule of thumb:** object storage is for bytes, not for rows. It has no index, no transaction, no partial update, and no cheap random write. It is excellent for the source of truth for large immutable files, cheap archival, and lakehouse landing zones, and it is the wrong place for a mutable 200-byte record.

**Questions**
1. What is an inverted index, and what is a posting list?
2. Why must the query analyzer and the index analyzer match, and what breaks when they do not?
3. What is an analysis chain, and what do character filters, tokenizers, and token filters do?
4. `keyword` vs `text`: state the decision rule.
5. Why are synonyms a deployment concern, and what is `synonym_graph` for?
6. What is BM25, and what do `k1` and `b` control?
7. When do you use field boosts, and when is a `function_score` decay better?
8. What is the difference between `must`, `should`, and `filter`, and why do filters belong in `filter`?
9. What is near-real-time search, what is the refresh interval, and how do you make a write immediately searchable?
10. What are `_source`, doc values, and stored fields, and when do you return only doc values?
11. What is dynamic mapping, what can it guess wrong, and what are the three `dynamic` settings?
12. What is mapping explosion, what causes it, and what are three fixes?
13. `object` vs `nested` mapping: the trade-off.
14. What is `ignore_above` on a keyword field, and what is the silent failure?
15. How do you pick the primary shard count?
16. What is the `doc_count_error_upper_bound`, and when must you distrust a facet?
17. Why is `from`/`size` capped, and what is the correct deep-pagination recipe?
18. Why does a non-deterministic sort break pagination?
19. What is `search_as_you_type`, and how does it beat a `prefix` query?
20. Walk through your relevance-debugging order.
21. When is Postgres full-text search the right answer instead of Elasticsearch?
22. OLTP vs OLAP: five differences.
23. What is a fact table's grain, and why must you be able to state it?
24. Slowly changing dimension types 1, 2, and 3, and when each is right.
25. Star vs snowflake: when do you denormalize dimensions?
26. What is a degenerate dimension and a factless fact table?
27. Why partition the fact table by date?
28. Batch vs micro-batch vs streaming ETL: when each?
29. ETL vs ELT, and why ELT won.
30. "Exactly once" — what do you actually get, and what do you build instead?
31. How do you handle late-arriving data? Watermarks, event time, and restatement.
32. What is CDC, what does it cost, and what is the WAL-slot failure mode?
33. How do you do an idempotent backfill?
34. What reconciliation queries would you run, and how often?
35. What are the `dbt`-style layering and the incremental strategies?
36. Multipart upload: the rules, and the cost leak.
37. Why is a multipart ETag not an MD5?
38. What do presigned URLs protect, and what must you constrain?
39. Which S3 operations are eventually consistent, and which are not?
40. Why is there no rename in object storage, and what pattern replaces it?
41. What does versioning cost, and what lifecycle rule must accompany it?
42. What is a range request good for?

**Model answer, Q30:** "Nobody gets exactly-once delivery end to end, and any design review where someone claims otherwise needs to be challenged, because there is a real reason: at-least-once delivery plus a non-transactional sink means a retry can duplicate the write, and the only way to be sure is a distributed transaction between the producer and the sink, which is expensive and usually impossible across systems. What you actually build is at-least-once delivery plus an idempotent sink. Concretely, the producer attaches a stable event id, the sink keeps a deduplication window keyed on that id, or the sink writes with a merge or upsert on a natural key such as `customer_id, day` so a repeated event overwrites itself. Then the pipeline is effectively-once in its *result* even though it is at-least-once in its *mechanism*, which is what you care about. Two related design rules fall out: the write must be commutative or at least last-write-wins with a deterministic key, so replaying an old event does not clobber a newer state, and the consumer checkpoint must be committed *after* the side effects are durable, not before, otherwise a crash loses work silently. And for the case where order actually matters, you partition the stream by key so a given entity's events are processed in order, and you accept that ordering is only guaranteed within a partition."

**Mistakes:** assuming a write is instantly searchable; `from`-based deep pagination; sorting on `_score` alone with a paginated request; aggregations on a `text` field; `keyword` without `ignore_above` thought; synonyms in a production index with no reindex plan; a non-deterministic sort; using `must` for filters; expecting `COUNT(*)` from a `terms` agg to be exact on a large index; claiming exactly-once; windowing on processing time; leaving incomplete multipart uploads alive forever; trusting a multipart ETag as an MD5; a presigned URL that lets the client choose the key.

**Mini task:** index 500k products with a deliberately dirty feed (missing fields, a field that is sometimes a number and sometimes a string, a field that is sometimes `null`) and watch dynamic mapping guess wrong; then set `dynamic: strict` and add templates, and index again. Build a query with a phrase filter, a `filter`-clause tenant predicate, a facet, and a `function_score` decay, and measure the difference between filters in `must` and in `filter`. Then implement `search_after` with a PIT and prove that pagination is stable while you keep indexing new documents. Finally, put a `flattened` field, a `nested` array of objects, and a `keyword` field in one mapping and write a query for each.

---

# 6.26 Data Modeling Exercises
Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition:** Six end-to-end modelling problems, each worked through with the same checklist, plus a deep dive on the booking overlap problem because it is a classic interview question with a real, elegant answer.

**The checklist every exercise answers:**
1. Requirements (and the non-requirements, which matter more)
2. Entities and keys
3. Relationships and cardinality
4. Normalization notes (where, and where to stop)
5. Indexes (with the reasoning, tied to the query shapes)
6. Access patterns with real queries
7. What to denormalize, and the freshness contract for each copy
8. Scaling path (what breaks first, and at what size)
9. Tenancy isolation
10. Deletion and retention
11. Two or three real SQL queries
12. One real MongoDB pipeline (showing when a document store is the better answer)

---

## 6.26.1 E-commerce catalogue, cart, and orders

**Requirements**
- Products with variants (size, colour), images, and a category hierarchy; category navigation; full-text search.
- Browsing must be fast; search must be typo-tolerant and faceted.
- Cart per user, mutable, persisted across devices, tolerant of seconds of staleness.
- Checkout must be exactly-once and must not oversell stock, oversell a coupon, or double-charge.
- Order history with immutable line-item snapshots (the price charged, the name shown).
- B2C only, single region initially, one currency, no returns for v1.

**Entities and keys**
```
products(id PK uuid, sku UNIQUE, title, description, status, category_id FK, created_at, deleted_at)
product_variants(id PK, product_id FK, sku UNIQUE, attributes jsonb, price_cents, currency)
inventory(product_variant_id PK/FK, available INT, reserved INT, version BIGINT)
categories(id PK, parent_id FK NULL, name, slug UNIQUE)
carts(id PK, user_id FK UNIQUE, updated_at)
cart_items(cart_id FK, product_variant_id, qty, CHECK qty > 0, PK(cart_id, product_variant_id))
orders(id PK, user_id FK, status, total_cents, currency, placed_at, payment_intent_id UNIQUE)
order_items(id PK, order_id FK, product_variant_id, sku_snapshot, name_snapshot,
            unit_price_cents, qty, line_total_cents)
payments(id PK, order_id FK UNIQUE, provider, provider_ref UNIQUE, amount_cents, status)
coupons(code PK, kind, value, max_uses, used_count, expires_at)
coupon_redemptions(coupon_code FK, user_id, order_id, PK(coupon_code, order_id))
```

**Relationships and cardinality**
- Product 1:N Variant (bounded in practice; if a product had unbounded variants, variant is a separate aggregate anyway).
- Category 1:N Category (self-referencing tree, depth 3 or so, kept flat with a `path` or `parent_id`).
- User 1:1 Cart; Cart 1:N CartItem; CartItem N:1 Variant.
- User 1:N Order; Order 1:N OrderItem; Order 1:1 Payment.
- Coupon M:N User, realised as `coupon_redemptions` (an association table, see 6.1–6.17 for why this is 3NF and why you still need it).

**Normalization notes**
- Fully 3NF in Postgres: no product attributes in the order item, no category names duplicated.
- The one deliberate violation is inside `order_items`: `sku_snapshot`, `name_snapshot`, `unit_price_cents`. That is not duplication, it is a *point-in-time record*, and it is the correct answer because an invoice must not change when the product is renamed or repriced. Label it as an immutable snapshot so nobody "helpfully" normalises it later.
- `product_variants.attributes` is a `jsonb` bag (`{size: 'L', colour: 'black'}`) because the attribute set varies by category and is queried with `@>`. That is the legitimate use of JSONB; a filter on it needs a GIN index and the queries are less optimizable than relational columns.

**Indexes**
```sql
-- browsing: category + status, newest first (equality prefix then sort, see 6.1-6.17)
CREATE INDEX ON products (category_id, created_at DESC) WHERE deleted_at IS NULL;
CREATE INDEX ON products (status, created_at DESC) WHERE deleted_at IS NULL;
-- the JSONB containment filter for variant attributes
CREATE INDEX ON product_variants USING gin (attributes jsonb_path_ops);
-- variant lookup by attribute (search facet) - a functional index into the JSONB
CREATE INDEX ON product_variants ((attributes->>'colour')) WHERE attributes ? 'colour';
-- order history
CREATE INDEX ON orders (user_id, placed_at DESC);
CREATE INDEX ON order_items (order_id);
CREATE INDEX ON order_items (product_variant_id);       -- "which orders contained this variant"
-- search: a GIN index keeps a Postgres full-text option open before 6.25 kicks in
ALTER TABLE products ADD COLUMN search_tsv tsvector
  GENERATED ALWAYS AS (setweight(to_tsvector('english', coalesce(title,'')), 'A')
                     || setweight(to_tsvector('english', coalesce(description,'')), 'B')) STORED;
CREATE INDEX ON products USING gin (search_tsv);
-- carts: one row per user
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX ON carts (user_id);
```

**Access patterns with real queries**
```sql
-- AP1: category page, newest first, paged (keyset, not OFFSET -- see 6.1-6.17)
SELECT id, title, price_cents
FROM products p
JOIN product_variants v ON v.product_id = p.id
WHERE p.category_id = $1 AND p.status = 'active' AND p.deleted_at IS NULL
  AND (p.created_at, p.id) < ($2, $3)            -- keyset cursor
ORDER BY p.created_at DESC, p.id DESC
LIMIT 24;

-- AP2: product detail with variants and live stock
SELECT p.id, p.title, p.description,
       v.id AS variant_id, v.sku, v.attributes, v.price_cents, i.available
FROM products p
LEFT JOIN product_variants v ON v.product_id = p.id
LEFT JOIN inventory i ON i.product_variant_id = v.id
WHERE p.sku = $1;

-- AP3: order history with a total
SELECT o.id, o.status, o.total_cents, o.placed_at, count(oi.id) AS line_count
FROM orders o LEFT JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
WHERE o.user_id = $1
GROUP BY o.id
ORDER BY o.placed_at DESC
LIMIT 20;

-- AP4: top products for a category in a window (the reporting query; note it is not served by the OLTP primary at peak)
SELECT oi.sku_snapshot, sum(oi.qty) AS units, sum(oi.line_total_cents) AS revenue
FROM order_items oi
JOIN orders o ON o.id = oi.order_id
WHERE o.placed_at >= $1 AND o.status IN ('paid','shipped') AND oi.sku_snapshot = ANY($2)
GROUP BY oi.sku_snapshot
ORDER BY revenue DESC
LIMIT 50;
```

**Denormalize (with a freshness contract)**
- `order_items` snapshots: immutable, no contract needed.
- `products.category_name`: denormalized onto the product so the category page is one read; refreshed on category rename by an event or a nightly job. Acceptable because it is display-only.
- `inventory.available`: authoritative in Postgres, mirrored to Redis for the "add to cart" check only; the authoritative decrement is in the transaction.
- A materialized `category_product_counts` table for the category navigation, refreshed on a schedule; not read on the critical path.
- Feed/summary documents in Mongo for a landing page that aggregates the newest product per category.

**Scaling path**
1. Reads scale with replicas; add PgBouncer before you add application instances.
2. The category page and search are the read-heavy paths: move search to Elasticsearch (6.25) and the category page to a CDN-cached JSON or Redis-cached payload.
3. Writes: the inventory decrement is the hotspot. Partition or shard by `product_variant_id` (or use a per-variant advisory lock); move to a sharded counter store if one variant's write rate exceeds a few thousand per second.
4. Order history: partition `orders` by month once it reaches tens of millions, and archive closed orders to object storage with a Parquet/Parquet-and-warehouse path.
5. Everything above 100M orders and no managed shard router: shard by `user_id` (so a user's orders are co-located) and accept that cross-user reporting moves to a warehouse.

**Tenancy isolation**
Single-tenant for v1, but design for it: put `tenant_id` on every table from day one, make it the leading column of every composite index, and enforce it in the data-access layer (a Prisma middleware or a Postgres RLS policy) so a forgotten `where` clause cannot leak across tenants. RLS plus `SET LOCAL app.tenant_id` per transaction is the version that does not rely on developer discipline. Rejected-tenant rows are `status = 'rejected'` in a `baskets` table with an exclusion constraint (6.26.3) rather than deleted, because a delete loses the audit trail.

**Deletion and retention**
- Soft delete products (`deleted_at`), hard delete cart items, and never hard delete an order (legal and accounting requirement).
- `inventory` rows are removed when a variant is retired, with `reserved = 0` enforced.
- GDPR: `users` rows are anonymised (scramble email, null the name) on request, with a `user_erasure_jobs` table so the erasure is itself auditable and retryable; order rows are retained with a pseudonymous `user_id` because tax law requires it, and that conflict is a real conversation, not a bug.
- Payment provider data is never stored; only a reference and a last4.

**The MongoDB pipeline for this domain (when a document store is genuinely better)**
```js
// Denormalized product detail document: variant + live stock + rating summary + facets.
// One read, no joins, a shape that changes per category.
db.productDetails.aggregate([
  { $match: { _id: 'p900' } },
  { $project: {
      _id: 0, title: 1, description: 1,
      variants: {
        $filter: { input: '$variants', as: 'v', cond: { $eq: ['$$v.status', 'active'] } }
      },
      priceRange: {
        min: { $min: '$variants.priceCents' },
        max: { $max: '$variants.priceCents' }
      },
      rating: { avg: '$rating.avg', count: '$rating.count' }
  } },
  { $lookup: {                      // the one join worth doing: live stock
      from: 'inventory',
      localField: '_id',
      foreignField: 'productId',
      as: 'stock'
  } },
  { $addFields: {
      variants: { $map: {
          input: '$variants', as: 'v',
          in: { $mergeObjects: ['$$v', {
            available: { $getField: { field: '$$v.id', input: { $arrayToObject: {
              $map: { input: '$stock', as: 's',
                      in: { k: '$$s.variantId', v: '$$s.available' } } } } } }
          }] }
      } }
  } },
  { $project: { stock: 0 } }
], { allowDiskUse: true, maxTimeMS: 200 });
```
**Indexes for the document version:** `{ status: 1, createdAt: -1 }` for category listing, `{ "variants.sku": 1 }` (multikey — remember the one-array-per-index rule from 6.19) for variant lookup, `{ "variants.attributes.colour": 1 }` for the facet, and a text index on `title` plus `description` if you search in Mongo rather than in Elasticsearch. The pipeline above is deliberately *worse* than the SQL for the reporting query (AP4) and *better* for the product detail read (AP2) — that asymmetry is the honest reason to consider a document store at all.

---

## 6.26.2 Social network: graph, feed, and fan-out

**Requirements**
- Follow/unfollow (directed), profiles, posts (text + media refs), reactions, comments (one level of nesting for v1).
- Two feed products: a global "for you" feed and a following feed. Read-heavy (100k reads/s), write-light.
- Feed freshness: seconds are acceptable. A reaction count that is 5 seconds stale is acceptable. A lost follow is not.
- Privacy: a private account's posts are visible only to approved followers.

**Entities and keys**
```
users(id PK, handle UNIQUE, display_name, avatar_url, is_private, follower_count BIGINT, following_count BIGINT, created_at)
follows(follower_id FK, followee_id FK, created_at, PK(follower_id, followee_id))
posts(id PK, author_id FK, body, visibility, media jsonb, created_at, deleted_at)
reactions(post_id FK, user_id FK, kind, created_at, PK(post_id, user_id))    -- one reaction per user per post
```
