```
comments(id PK, post_id FK, author_id FK, body, created_at, deleted_at)
post_counts(post_id PK, reactions BIGINT, comments BIGINT)                  -- denormalized
```

**Relationships and cardinality**
- User M:N User via `follows` (a directed, self-referencing adjacency list — the "join table that is a graph edge", see 6.22). PK on `(follower_id, followee_id)` gives the forward query; a separate index on `followee_id` gives the reverse ("who follows me") and is mandatory.
- Post 1:N Comment; Post M:N User via `reactions` with the reaction kind as a non-key attribute (a PK on `(post_id, user_id)` means a user cannot switch from a like to a dislike; if that is required, PK on `(post_id, user_id, kind)` and accept duplicates, or make the key `(post_id, user_id)` and update the kind).
- User 1:N Post, User 1:N Comment.

**Normalization notes**
- 3NF except the two count columns and the feed tables below.
- `follows` must not be given a surrogate `id`; the composite PK *is* the identity, and it makes the follow an naturally idempotent.
- `media` as `jsonb` with a `[{kind, url, width, height, alt}]` shape: bounded (max 10 per post) and never independently queried, so embedding is right (see 6.19.2).
- Media bytes are in object storage, referenced by key; the database stores the metadata (6.25.5).

**Indexes**
```sql
CREATE INDEX ON follows (followee_id, follower_id);       -- reverse direction
CREATE INDEX ON posts (author_id, created_at DESC) WHERE deleted_at IS NULL;
CREATE INDEX ON posts (created_at DESC) WHERE visibility = 'public' AND deleted_at IS NULL;
CREATE INDEX ON comments (post_id, created_at);
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX ON users (lower(handle));             -- case-insensitive handle uniqueness
```
The partial indexes on `posts` matter: at 90% of rows being `visibility = 'public'`, a partial index is both much smaller and a better fit for the dominant query.

**Access patterns with real queries**
```sql
-- AP1: my following feed, keyset paged
SELECT p.id, p.author_id, p.body, p.created_at
FROM posts p
JOIN follows f ON f.follower_id = $1 AND f.followee_id = p.author_id
WHERE p.deleted_at IS NULL
  AND (p.created_at, p.id) < ($2, $3)
ORDER BY p.created_at DESC, p.id DESC
LIMIT 30;
-- This is the NESTED LOOP you must watch: with 500 follows it reads 500 index
-- ranges and sorts. It is fine at 500 and terrible at 50,000. The fan-out
-- materialisation below exists precisely because of this query.

-- AP2: who follows me (reverse), with count
SELECT count(*) FROM follows WHERE followee_id = $1;
SELECT u.id, u.handle, u.display_name, f.created_at
FROM follows f JOIN users u ON u.id = f.follower_id
WHERE f.followee_id = $1 ORDER BY f.created_at DESC LIMIT 50;

-- AP3: post detail with reaction count from the denormalized table
SELECT p.id, p.body, p.author_id, u.handle, pc.reactions, pc.comments
FROM posts p
JOIN users u ON u.id = p.author_id
LEFT JOIN post_counts pc ON pc.post_id = p.id
WHERE p.id = $1;

-- AP4: recommended users (people followed by people I follow, minus people I already follow)
WITH mine AS (SELECT followee_id FROM follows WHERE follower_id = $1),
     theirs AS (SELECT DISTINCT f.followee_id
                FROM follows f JOIN mine ON mine.followee_id = f.follower_id
                WHERE f.followee_id <> $1)
SELECT u.id, count(*) AS mutuals
FROM theirs JOIN users u ON u.id = theirs.followee_id
WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM follows WHERE follower_id = $1 AND followee_id = theirs.followee_id)
GROUP BY u.id ORDER BY mutuals DESC LIMIT 20;
```

**Denormalize (with contracts)**
- **Fan-out on write (push model):** on post creation, insert a row per follower into `feed_entries(follower_id, post_id, created_at, PK(follower_id, created_at, post_id))`. Read is a single index range scan, which is why it exists. Contract: eventual, seconds to a minute behind; a new post is not in your feed instantly.
- **Fan-out on read (pull model):** read-time merge of the following feed with a ranked global feed. Contract: fresher, but the query is expensive at scale and needs a ranker.
- **Hybrid (the real answer):** push to followers, and for high-fanout authors (>10,000 followers) pull instead and mark the entry as "out-of-network". This is the standard industry fix and naming it is a strong signal.
- `follower_count` / `following_count`: denormalized, updated with `UPDATE users SET follower_count = follower_count + 1 WHERE id = $1` in the same transaction as the `follows` insert. Contract: display only, never used for authorization or billing; reconciled nightly from `count(*)`.
- `post_counts`: same treatment, updated by the reaction/insert path.
- Comments: subset pattern — the post document (in Mongo) embeds the 3 newest comments and a `commentCount`; older comments are a separate collection.

**Scaling path**
1. `follows` and `posts` are the hot tables; they shard by `author_id` (co-locates a user's posts and, with care, their follows).
2. `feed_entries` is the volume monster: one row per follower per post. At 1M DAU and 5 posts each, that is 5M rows/day, so partition by day and drop old partitions; index `(follower_id, created_at DESC)`.
3. Reaction counts move to a per-post counter service (Redis + periodic flush) once writes exceed what one primary can take.
4. Recommendation goes to a batch pipeline producing a nightly `user_recommendations(user_id, candidate_id, score)` table — a materialized view, not a query.
5. Global feed ranking becomes a precomputed top-N per time bucket plus a random tiebreak for exploration.

**Tenancy isolation**
Not multi-tenant, but privacy is the same problem. Enforce visibility in the query, not in application code alone: a Postgres RLS policy on `posts` that checks `visibility = 'public' OR author_id = current_setting('app.user_id')::uuid OR EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM follows WHERE follower_id = current_setting('app.user_id')::uuid AND followee_id = posts.author_id)`. That is expensive per row, so the pragmatic version is a view that joins against a materialized "can I see this" set for the current author, or a dedicated `post_visibility` table keyed `(post_id, viewer_id)` for private accounts only. Do the RLS on the *write* path and a cheap predicate on the read path.

**Deletion and retention**
- Account deletion is the hard case: posts with reactions and comments from other users. Policy: the author is pseudonymised (`display_name = 'Deleted user'`, email and tokens destroyed), posts either soft-deleted or retained per a content policy, and the follow edges hard-deleted (a `follows` row for a nonexistent user is pure garbage). Do this asynchronously in a queue with an idempotent job.
- GDPR erasure: comments authored by the user are pseudonymised, not deleted, when a post is public and someone else commented on it — a legal conflict worth naming in an interview.
- Feed entries: partition by day, keep 7 days, because nobody scrolls a week-old aggregated feed.

**The MongoDB pipeline (feed + graph, one round trip)**
```js
// Bounded-depth follow-of-follow discovery on the edge collection, then rank.
db.follows.aggregate([
  { $match: { followerId: { $in: ['u1','u2','u3'] } } },     // people I follow (chunked)
  { $group: { _id: '$followeeId', mutuals: { $sum: 1 } } },   // collapse to candidates
  { $match: { _id: { $nin: ['u1','u2','u3'] } } },
  { $lookup: {                                                   // hydrate profiles
      from: 'users',
      localField: '_id', foreignField: '_id', as: 'profile'
  } },
  { $unwind: '$profile' },
  { $addFields: {
      score: { $add: [
        { $multiply: ['$mutuals', 10] },
        { $ln: { $add: [1, { $ifNull: ['$profile.followerCount', 0] } } } }
      ] }
  } },
  { $sort: { score: -1 } },
  { $limit: 20 },
  { $project: { _id: 1, score: 1,
                handle: '$profile.handle', displayName: '$profile.displayName' } }
], { allowDiskUse: true, maxTimeMS: 300 });
```
`$graphLookup` is the alternative when the depth is not known, and its `maxDepth` is exactly the line where you decide the relational recursive CTE (6.22.3) is the better tool.

---

## 6.26.3 Booking and reservation with overlap prevention

**Requirements**
- A bookable resource (a hotel room, a meeting room, a doctor, a rental car) has a calendar.
- A reservation occupies a half-open interval `[start, end)`.
- Two reservations for the same resource must never overlap. This is the hard invariant.
- A reservation has states: `held` (a temporary hold while the user pays), `confirmed`, `cancelled`, `expired`.
- Hold expiry: a hold that is not confirmed within N minutes releases the slot.
- Overbooking by *quantity* is a different requirement (there are 10 identical seats); handle it with a counter and a constraint, not with an interval index.
- Concurrent booking attempts for the same slot are the normal case, not the exception.

**Entities and keys**
```
resources(id PK, kind, name, timezone, capacity INT DEFAULT 1)
reservations(id PK, resource_id FK, user_id FK, status, starts_at timestamptz,
             ends_at timestamptz, hold_expires_at, created_at,
             EXCLUDE USING gist (resource_id WITH =, tstzrange(starts_at, ends_at, '[)') WITH &&)
             WHERE (status IN ('held','confirmed')))
resource_slots -- optional precomputed view of availability
```
**Cardinality:** Resource 1:N Reservation; User 1:N Reservation. The `tstzrange` is generated in Postgres from the two timestamps, and `[)` (half-open, exclusive end) is the right interval semantics — `[10:00, 11:00)` and `[11:00, 12:00)` do not overlap, while `[]` would consider them overlapping. Choosing `[)` avoids the off-by-one double-booking that `[` and `]` produce at the boundary.

**Worked walkthrough of the overlap problem.** This is the classic interview question, so here is the reasoning out loud.

*Attempt 1 — application-level check-then-insert:*
```sql
SELECT 1 FROM reservations
WHERE resource_id = $1 AND status = 'confirmed'
  AND starts_at < $3 AND ends_at > $2;    -- the overlap predicate
-- if no rows, INSERT
```
This is **wrong**, and the reason is the classic TOCTOU race: two concurrent transactions both see no overlap and both insert. A `SERIALIZABLE` transaction or `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` on the resource row can fix the race, but you have now serialised every booking attempt for that resource and you must still handle the serializable-retry path. Correct, but the most code for the least insight.

*Attempt 2 — advisory lock per resource:*
```sql
SELECT pg_advisory_xact_lock(hashtextextended($1::text, 0));   -- resource id, transaction-scoped
-- then the check-then-insert as above, now safe
```
Safe, one line, auto-released on rollback. Cost: all bookings for one resource serialise, and a crash cannot leak the lock (it is transaction-scoped, which is the point). For a resource with a modest booking rate this is the pragmatic production answer, and it is worth offering even though the exclusion constraint is the better answer.

*Attempt 3 — an exclusion constraint (the elegant answer):*
```sql
ALTER TABLE reservations ADD CONSTRAINT no_overlap
  EXCLUDE USING gist (
    resource_id WITH =,
    tstzrange(starts_at, ends_at, '[)') WITH &&
  ) WHERE (status IN ('held', 'confirmed'));
```
The database itself then refuses an overlapping pair, and it does so atomically, with no application check, no lock, and no race. The `&&` operator is interval overlap; `WITH =` on the resource id makes the constraint per-resource; and the `WHERE` clause (a *partial* exclusion constraint) is essential, because cancelled and expired reservations must be allowed to overlap freely. Prerequisite: `CREATE EXTENSION btree_gist;` so that a scalar (`bigint`) can participate in a GiST index alongside the range. The error you get back is `23P01 exclusion_violation`, which you map to a clean 409 Conflict with the conflicting interval in the message.
```sql
-- the index that makes it fast and keeps GiST happy
CREATE INDEX ON reservations USING gist (resource_id, tstzrange(starts_at, ends_at, '[)'));
```
*Why the three-argument form matters on Postgres:* the `EXCLUDE` constraint takes an `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` lock to add and validates existing rows, so on a large table add it `NOT VALID` (Postgres supports this for exclusion constraints) and then `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT`, which only takes a lock that permits reads and writes. Add `lock_timeout` to the session. This is the exact pattern from 6.24.8, applied to a constraint rather than a column.

*Attempt 4 — serializable transactions as the general answer:* set the isolation level to `SERIALIZABLE`, keep the check-then-insert, and retry on SQLSTATE `40001`. Correct for *any* invariant, including ones an exclusion constraint cannot express ("at most 3 confirmed reservations for this user in this hour"). Slower under contention, and the retry logic must be idempotent. Cross-reference 6.1–6.17 for isolation levels and retry discipline.

*Attempt 5 — the "capacity" variant (many identical units):* if the resource has 10 identical seats, intervals are the wrong model. Use a counter and a conditional update:
```sql
UPDATE resources SET available = available - $1
WHERE id = $1 AND available >= $1
RETURNING available;                    -- 0 rows means sold out; no race
```
The `WHERE available >= $1` makes it a single atomic compare-and-swap, and `RETURNING` tells you the new value for optimistic UI. Then a separate `reservations` table records the individual bookings without an exclusion constraint. This is the "counter with a guard" pattern from 6.24.7, and it is the right model for inventory, seats, and quota.

*Which answer to lead with:* "The exclusion constraint, because it moves the invariant into the database, where it cannot be bypassed by a code path someone forgot. I would mention `btree_gist` and the partial `WHERE status IN ('held','confirmed')`, and I would add it `NOT VALID` then `VALIDATE` on a large table. If I were on an engine without exclusion constraints — MySQL, Mongo — I would use a transactionally-held lock: a Postgres advisory lock if I have Postgres, or a `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` on the resource row, and I would never rely on an application-level check alone."

**Hold lifecycle.** A hold is a reservation with `status = 'held'` and a `hold_expires_at`; it occupies the slot (so it is inside the exclusion constraint) and is released by a sweeper. Two designs: a periodic job (`UPDATE reservations SET status = 'expired' WHERE status = 'held' AND hold_expires_at < now()`) or, better, a `tstzrange(starts_at, least(ends_at, hold_expires_at), '[)')` expression so the slot frees itself at the hold deadline without any job — at the cost of a more complex constraint. The sweeper needs a partial index on `(hold_expires_at) WHERE status = 'held'`. And the payment callback must be **idempotent and conditional**: `UPDATE reservations SET status = 'confirmed' WHERE id = $1 AND status = 'held' AND hold_expires_at > now()` — 0 rows updated means the hold already expired, and you must then refund. This is the single most common real bug in booking systems: paying for an expired hold.

**Indexes**
```sql
-- availability lookup for a calendar view: the same range query, index-supported
CREATE INDEX ON reservations (resource_id, starts_at) WHERE status IN ('held','confirmed');
-- the sweeper
CREATE INDEX ON reservations (hold_expires_at) WHERE status = 'held';
-- user history
CREATE INDEX ON reservations (user_id, starts_at DESC);
-- the GiST index required by the exclusion constraint (btree_gist for the scalar part)
CREATE INDEX ON reservations USING gist (resource_id, tstzrange(starts_at, ends_at, '[)'));
```

**Access patterns with real queries**
```sql
-- AP1: availability for a day (overlap check, index-supported)
SELECT r.id, r.starts_at, r.ends_at
FROM reservations r
WHERE r.resource_id = $1
  AND r.status IN ('held','confirmed')
  AND tstzrange(r.starts_at, r.ends_at, '[)') && tstzrange($2::timestamptz, $3::timestamptz, '[)')
ORDER BY r.starts_at;

-- AP2: attempt to book -- the whole invariant in one statement
INSERT INTO reservations (id, resource_id, user_id, status, starts_at, ends_at, hold_expires_at)
VALUES ($1, $2, $3, 'held', $4, $5, now() + interval '10 minutes')
ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING;     -- note: conflicts on PK only, not on EXCLUDE -- see below
-- the EXCLUDE violation raises 23P01; catch it and return 409

-- AP3: confirm a hold after payment (idempotent and expiry-safe)
UPDATE reservations
SET status = 'confirmed', payment_ref = $2
WHERE id = $1 AND status = 'held' AND hold_expires_at > now();
-- 0 rows => expired => refund, do not confirm

-- AP4: cancel (releases the slot immediately because the partial constraint excludes it)
UPDATE reservations SET status = 'cancelled' WHERE id = $1 AND status IN ('held','confirmed');
```
A subtlety worth mentioning: `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` does **not** catch an exclusion-constraint violation (that is not a unique-index conflict), so the error path is a `23P01` catch, and in some drivers it surfaces as a generic `QueryFailedError` with `code = '23P01'`. Good detail to have ready.

**Normalization notes**
- Fully 3NF. The only derived state is the availability query, which is not stored.
- Do not store a `booked_dates` string or a bitmask per day: it is redundant, it disagrees with the reservations under concurrency, and it does not generalise to arbitrary intervals.

**Denormalize (with contracts)**
- A `resource_availability` cache keyed `(resource_id, day)` in Redis holding the list of taken intervals; TTL 30–60s with jitter. Contract: a slot may appear free when it is taken, and never the reverse. A booking attempt always re-checks in the database, so the cache is safe in that direction.
- For a very high-volume, low-contention case, a precomputed per-day capacity counter per resource (see the counter variant above).

**Scaling path**
1. The exclusion constraint is the scalability ceiling: two bookings for the same resource serialise on the GiST entry. That is correct and usually fine, because a single resource's booking rate is bounded by human behaviour.
2. Shard by `resource_id` when one database cannot hold the constraint across all resources — and note that this is a genuinely good sharding key for this domain, since the invariant is *within* a resource.
3. Very high volume across many independent resources: partition by `resource_id` hash, or move to per-resource authority (one shard owns a resource's calendar).
4. Global calendar search ("any room in Berlin for 3 nights") needs a secondary structure — a per-city, per-day inverted index of availability, refreshed on write.

**Tenancy isolation**
Multi-property marketplace: `resource_id` is already tenant-scoped, so the constraint is naturally per resource. Add `tenant_id` to the constraint columns if resources can be re-parented across tenants, and always scope the availability query by tenant so a user cannot see another property's bookings.

**Deletion and retention**
- Reservations are financial records: never hard-delete. Cancel, and keep for the statutory period (7 years in many jurisdictions).
- Guest PII is separated into a `reservation_guests` table so a booking record can be retained with the guest details erased.
- Holds expire and are retained for 30 days for debugging, then archived to object storage.
- The sweeper must be idempotent and safe to run concurrently (two sweepers running at once is fine — the `WHERE status = 'held'` predicate makes the second a no-op).

**The MongoDB pipeline (availability + conflict detection)**
```js
// 1) find overlapping holds/confirmed bookings for a resource
db.reservations.aggregate([
  { $match: {
      resourceId: 'r42',
      status: { $in: ['held', 'confirmed'] },
      startsAt: { $lt: ISODate('2026-03-02T10:00:00Z') },
      endsAt:   { $gt: ISODate('2026-03-01T10:00:00Z') }
  } },
  { $project: { _id: 0, startsAt: 1, endsAt: 1, status: 1, holdExpiresAt: 1 } },
], { maxTimeMS: 200 });
// 2) the write, with the invariant enforced by a unique index on a canonical key
//    rather than by an interval index, which Mongo does not have:
//
//    db.reservations.createIndex({ resourceId: 1, day: 1, slot: 1 },
//                                { unique: true, partialFilterExpression: { status: { $in: [...] } } });
//
//    Model each bookable slot (30-min granularity) as a field in an embedded array
//    `slots: [{ start, end, reservationId }]`, and use a transaction to check-and-claim:
//
//    const session = client.startSession();
//    await session.withTransaction(async () => {
//      const res = await db.reservations.findOneAndUpdate(
//        { _id: 'r42', status: 'active', 'slots': { $not: { $elemMatch: {
//            start: { $lt: end }, end: { $gt: start }, reservationId: null } } } },
//        { $set: { 'slots.$[s].reservationId': newId } },
//        { arrayFilters: [{ 's.start': { $lt: end }, 's.end': { $gt: start } }], session }
//      );
//      if (!res) throw new Conflict();
//      await db.bookings.insertOne({ ... }, { session });
//    });
```
**The honest conclusion to state out loud:** MongoDB has no exclusion constraint, so the interval-overlap invariant must be modelled as a *discrete slot grid* with a unique index, or checked inside a transaction. That is a real modelling cost, and for a domain whose central invariant is interval overlap, Postgres is the better answer. The document store wins on the *availability read* (one document, one read) and loses on the *correctness write*.

---

## 6.26.4 Chat: conversations, messages, presence, and delivery

**Requirements**
- One-to-one and group conversations; messages in order per conversation; delivery state per recipient.
- Recipients see new messages within a second (push via WebSocket/SSE), offline messages delivered on reconnect.
- Message history paginated, with edits, deletes (tombstones), and reactions.
- Attachments in object storage.
- Fan-out to many recipients (a group of 5,000) must not block the sender.

**Entities and keys**
```
conversations(id PK, kind, title, avatar_url, created_at, last_message_at)
conversation_members(conversation_id FK, user_id FK, role, last_read_message_id NULL, joined_at,
                     PK(conversation_id, user_id))
messages(id PK, conversation_id FK, sender_id FK, seq BIGINT, body, reply_to NULL,
         edited_at NULL, deleted_at NULL, created_at,
         UNIQUE(conversation_id, seq))          -- the ordering invariant
message_reactions(message_id FK, user_id FK, kind, PK(message_id, user_id, kind))
attachments(id PK, message_id FK, object_key, size, content_type, checksum, width, height)
```
**Cardinality:** Conversation M:N User via `conversation_members`; Conversation 1:N Message; Message 1:N Attachment; Message M:N User via `message_reactions`.

**The ordering decision is the whole design.** Two options:
1. **Database sequence per conversation** (`seq` from a counter service or from a `SERIAL` on a per-conversation table). Ordering is authoritative, gapless, and you can page with `seq < cursor`. Cost: an extra round trip or a contended counter on the hot path.
2. **Client-generated sortable ids** (UUIDv7 or a Snowflake-style id with timestamp + conversation + counter). Ordering is derived from the id, no round trip, and ids are unique across clients. Cost: ids from different senders can be slightly out of order (clock skew), and there can be gaps.

The real systems answer: the **server assigns a monotonic sequence per conversation** (a Redis `INCR` per conversation, or a Postgres sequence if the conversation is low volume), because "who said what in what order" is a correctness question, not a convenience. And the id remains globally unique for deduplication. If you use server-assigned sequences, the invariant `UNIQUE(conversation_id, seq)` is what makes a duplicate or a reordering impossible to persist.

**Indexes**
```sql
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX ON messages (conversation_id, seq);         -- also the paging index
CREATE INDEX ON conversation_members (user_id, last_read_message_id);  -- my inbox / unread counts
CREATE INDEX ON conversation_members (conversation_id, user_id);        -- PK covers it, but confirm
CREATE INDEX ON attachments (message_id);
CREATE INDEX ON messages (created_at DESC) WHERE deleted_at IS NULL;  -- retention sweep
```
Plus one denormalized per-conversation table for the inbox:
```sql
-- user_conversations: the "chat list", denormalized so the inbox is a single index scan
CREATE TABLE user_conversations (
  user_id uuid NOT NULL,
  conversation_id uuid NOT NULL,
  last_message_id uuid NOT NULL,
  last_message_at timestamptz NOT NULL,
  unread_count int NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  muted boolean NOT NULL DEFAULT false,
  PRIMARY KEY (user_id, conversation_id)
);
CREATE INDEX ON user_conversations (user_id, last_message_at DESC);
```

**Access patterns with real queries**
```sql
-- AP1: message history, keyset paged (never OFFSET: messages arrive while you page)
SELECT m.id, m.seq, m.sender_id, m.body, m.created_at, m.edited_at
FROM messages m
WHERE m.conversation_id = $1 AND m.seq < $2
ORDER BY m.seq DESC
LIMIT 50;

-- AP2: the inbox, one read
SELECT c.id, c.kind, c.title, uc.last_message_at, uc.unread_count, uc.muted
FROM user_conversations uc
JOIN conversations c ON c.id = uc.conversation_id
WHERE uc.user_id = $1
ORDER BY uc.last_message_at DESC
LIMIT 30;

-- AP3: unread count
SELECT coalesce(sum(unread_count), 0) AS total_unread
FROM user_conversations WHERE user_id = $1 AND muted = false;

-- AP4: mark read (idempotent, and monotonic -- never move the pointer backwards)
UPDATE conversation_members
SET last_read_message_id = $3
WHERE conversation_id = $1 AND user_id = $2
  AND coalesce(last_read_message_id::bigint, 0) < $4;   -- compare on the sequence
```
AP4's predicate is the subtle part: reading the conversation must never make unread messages reappear, so the update must be monotonic on the sequence, not on the timestamp (clock skew) and not unconditionally.

**Denormalize (with contracts)**
- `unread_count` per member: incremented on send to every member, reset to 0 on read. Contract: approximate; a crashed increment loses a count, reconciled by a periodic job that recomputes `count(*) WHERE seq > last_read_message_id` for active users. A wrong unread badge is a cosmetic bug; a wrong *permission* is not.
- Last-message preview on the conversation: denormalized from the newest message.
- Message search: not on the OLTP primary at all — index into Elasticsearch (6.25).

**Scaling path**
1. **The fan-out is the problem.** A message to 5,000 members means 5,000 `user_conversations` updates. Solution: cap synchronous fan-out (do it for the first N members, or only for members currently online, detected by presence in Redis) and enqueue the rest for a background worker with batched upserts. Mark the conversation as "delayed" so the UI can say "delivering…".
2. **Presence and typing indicators** in Redis with a short TTL (25s heartbeat, 2x TTL expiry). Never in the database.
3. **Message storage** partitions by `conversation_id` hash, or by month once the volume demands it. Conversation history on cold storage goes to object storage with a "load older messages" path.
4. **Read receipts** (`last_read_message_id` per member) are the highest-churn rows in the system; batch them and write at most once every few seconds per member.
5. Push notifications: a durable queue per platform, with per-device tokens, deduplicated by message id.

**Tenancy isolation**
A chat is naturally tenant-scoped: `conversation_members` is the ACL, and every read joins against it. The failure to avoid is a message fetch by id that skips the membership check — enforce it in the repository/RLS policy, not in the handler. Direct messages should additionally be unreadable by any operator role unless a legal hold exists, which is a policy question worth raising.

**Deletion and retention**
- Message deletion is a **tombstone**: set `deleted_at` and render a placeholder, so per-conversation `seq` never changes and history stays consistent. Hard-deleting a message would shift nothing (seq is a column) but would break the `last_read_message_id` cursor semantics and any client that already has the id.
- Account deletion: remove memberships, tombstone authored messages (policy-dependent), and delete the notification queue entries.
- Retention: messages 7 years for business, 30 days for ephemeral channels, with a partition drop per month and an archive to object storage (Parquet) for anything analytics needs.
- Media: object storage lifecycle deletes orphaned attachments (message deleted, attachment not) via a nightly reconciliation job.

**The MongoDB pipeline (search, moderation, and the timeline)**
```js
// Conversations with their last 3 messages, unread count, and a moderation flag
db.conversations.aggregate([
  { $match: { memberIds: 'u7', lastMessageAt: { $gte: ISODate('2026-01-01') } } },
  { $lookup: {
      from: 'messages',
      let: { cid: '$_id' },
      pipeline: [
        { $match: { $expr: { $eq: ['$conversationId', '$$cid'] } } },
        { $sort: { seq: -1 } },
        { $limit: 3 },
        { $project: { _id: 0, senderId: 1, body: 1, createdAt: 1, seq: 1 } }
      ],
      as: 'preview'
  } },
  { $project: {
      title: 1, lastMessageAt: 1,
      preview: { $reverseArray: '$preview' },        // oldest -> newest for display
      unread: {
        $size: {
          $filter: {
            input: '$allSeq',
            as: 's',
            cond: { $gt: ['$$s', { $ifNull: ['$$lastReadSeq', 0] }] }
          }
        }
      }
  } },
  { $sort: { lastMessageAt: -1 } },
  { $limit: 30 }
], { allowDiskUse: true, maxTimeMS: 500 });
```
Note the embedded `allSeq` array used for the unread count: that is a **bounded** array (the last few hundred sequence numbers) so embedding is legitimate (6.19.2), and it is exactly the kind of denormalization that must be capped, because an unbounded message list in a conversation document will hit the 16MB limit. The `let`/`pipeline` form of `$lookup` is used instead of the plain form so the index on `conversationId + seq` is actually used.

---

## 6.26.5 Multi-tenant SaaS

**Requirements**
- Many tenants (companies) on one platform. Tenant sizes differ by three orders of magnitude.
- Strict isolation: no cross-tenant data leakage, ever. Compliance requirements for some tenants.
- Per-tenant configuration, per-tenant feature flags, per-tenant rate limits, per-tenant encryption keys for the strictest tier.
- Per-tenant database credentials, audit logs, and data export/deletion on request.
- Efficient small queries; large tenants must be able to move to dedicated infrastructure.

**Three isolation models, honestly compared**

| Model | Isolation | Cost | Per-tenant query | Ops | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Shared tables, `tenant_id` column** | Weakest (one missing predicate leaks) | Cheapest; best density | Every query needs the predicate; index must lead with `tenant_id` | One database | Right for the majority of products |
| **Schema-per-tenant** | Strong logical isolation | Migrations × N; connection-pool and catalogue bloat | Fully indexed, no predicate needed | N schema migrations per deploy | Acceptable up to a few hundred tenants |
| **Database-per-tenant** | Strongest | N databases, N pools, N backups, N restore paths | Fully isolated | The real cost: a fleet | Only for enterprise tiers or regulatory needs |
| **Hybrid (the industry answer)** | Tier-dependent | A routing layer and two code paths to keep honest | Same code, different connection string | Two tiers to support | Ship this, and say so in the interview |

**Entities and keys** (shared-tables model)
```
tenants(id PK, slug UNIQUE, name, plan, status, created_at, region, settings jsonb)
users(id PK, tenant_id FK NOT NULL, email, password_hash, role, created_at, deleted_at,
      UNIQUE(tenant_id, email))
api_keys(id PK, tenant_id FK, key_hash UNIQUE, scopes, expires_at, last_used_at)
audit_log(id bigserial PK, tenant_id FK, actor_user_id, action, entity_type, entity_id,
          before jsonb, after jsonb, ip, created_at)
feature_flags(tenant_id, flag_key, enabled, config jsonb, PRIMARY KEY(tenant_id, flag_key))
-- every business table starts with:
orders(id PK, tenant_id uuid NOT NULL, ...)
```
Note `UNIQUE(tenant_id, email)` and not `UNIQUE(email)`. Global email uniqueness is a product decision (often a login-by-email requirement), and either way the index must be tenant-scoped so a query for one tenant's email is an index lookup on a narrow range.

**The two rules that make it safe**
1. **`tenant_id` is the leading column of every composite index.** `CREATE INDEX ON orders (tenant_id, created_at DESC)` serves both the tenant filter and the sort, and keeps one tenant's rows physically clustered so a large tenant does not evict everyone else's pages from the buffer pool. (Cross-reference 6.1–6.17: the ESR rule is unchanged; the equality prefix here is always the tenant.)
2. **The tenant predicate is injected, not remembered.** Options, in increasing order of strength: an application data layer that adds the predicate (Prisma middleware or extension, a Drizzle helper, a repository base class); a Postgres session variable plus **row-level security**:
   ```sql
   ALTER TABLE orders ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;
   ALTER TABLE orders FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;   -- so the table owner is subject to it too
   CREATE POLICY orders_tenant_isolation ON orders
     USING (tenant_id = current_setting('app.tenant_id')::uuid)
     WITH CHECK (tenant_id = current_setting('app.tenant_id')::uuid);
   -- per transaction:
   BEGIN; SET LOCAL app.tenant_id = 't-123'; ... COMMIT;
   ```
   RLS is the only version that catches the forgotten `where` clause, and its cost is real (a per-row predicate on every scan and every plan), so pair it with the composite indexes. A connection-pooler that does not support session variables per transaction (some transaction-pooling modes) will break `SET LOCAL` semantics — a genuinely tricky operational detail worth naming.

**Indexes**
```sql
CREATE INDEX ON orders (tenant_id, created_at DESC);
CREATE INDEX ON orders (tenant_id, status, created_at DESC);          -- the ESR order again
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX ON users (tenant_id, lower(email));
CREATE INDEX ON audit_log (tenant_id, created_at DESC);
CREATE INDEX ON audit_log (tenant_id, entity_type, entity_id);        -- "show me this record's history"
CREATE INDEX ON api_keys (key_hash) WHERE revoked_at IS NULL;        -- partial: revoked keys excluded
```

**Access patterns with real queries**
```sql
-- AP1: tenant dashboard, current period, paginated
SELECT date_trunc('day', placed_at) AS day, count(*), sum(total_cents)
FROM orders
WHERE tenant_id = $1 AND placed_at >= $2 AND placed_at < $3 AND status <> 'cancelled'
GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY 1;

-- AP2: search a tenant's users by email prefix
SELECT id, email, role, created_at FROM users
WHERE tenant_id = $1 AND email LIKE $2 || '%' AND deleted_at IS NULL
ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 25;
-- with a trigram index for real prefix search at scale:
-- CREATE EXTENSION pg_trgm;
-- CREATE INDEX ON users USING gin ((email::text) gin_trgm_ops);

-- AP3: per-tenant audit trail
SELECT created_at, actor_user_id, action, entity_type, entity_id, after
FROM audit_log
WHERE tenant_id = $1 AND created_at > $2
ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 100;

-- AP4: per-tenant usage metering, batched
INSERT INTO usage_daily (tenant_id, day, api_calls, bytes_out)
VALUES ($1, $2, $3, $4)
ON CONFLICT (tenant_id, day) DO UPDATE
  SET api_calls = usage_daily.api_calls + EXCLUDED.api_calls,
      bytes_out = usage_daily.bytes_out + EXCLUDED.bytes_out;
```

**What to denormalize**
- `tenants.settings` and `feature_flags` as `jsonb`: the shape varies per plan and per tenant, it is read as a whole, and it is never joined. Exactly the right use of JSONB.
- A `tenant_usage_daily` rollup for billing: computed by a scheduled job from the audit/usage tables, never queried live for a dashboard.
- A per-tenant `search_document` collection in Mongo, one document per tenant holding its search-visible entities, if you use a document store as a read model.

**Scaling path**
1. **Per-tenant noise neighbours.** A single 10,000-row-per-second tenant starves the rest. Isolation mechanisms in order: separate connection pool and `statement_timeout` per tier, per-tenant rate limiting (6.20.6), and eventually moving that tenant to a dedicated database. Route by a `tenants.region`/`tier` column resolved at connection time, cached in Redis with a short TTL so a migration does not require a deploy.
2. **Table bloat:** with 10,000 tenants, `pg_stat_user_tables` and index sizes become the real bottleneck. Partition by tenant hash when the count justifies it, or move the largest tenants out.
3. **Migration cost:** with database-per-tenant, migrations fan out and need a resumable runner with per-tenant state and a canary. This is an operations project, not a query.
4. **Query performance:** make sure no plan is a sequential scan of the whole table filtered by tenant; a "tenant filter" without a leading index column is the classic multi-tenant performance bug.

**Tenancy isolation (the checklist version)**
- `tenant_id NOT NULL` on every business table, with a FK to `tenants` where the lifecycle allows it.
- Every composite index leads with `tenant_id`.
- RLS as the safety net, `FORCE`d.
- Uniqueness scoped per tenant.
- Background jobs carry `tenant_id` and set the session variable per unit of work; a job that forgets it either does nothing or (worse) processes every tenant.
- Caches are keyed by tenant first: `t:{tenantId}:orders:{id}`. A cache key without the tenant prefix is a cross-tenant leak waiting for a hash collision or a copy-paste error.
- Object storage prefixes per tenant: `tenants/{tenantId}/...`, and presigned URLs restricted to that prefix.
- Object-level authorization is still checked: a `tenant_id` predicate is not a permission check. Use-case-specific checks belong in the application layer.

**Deletion and retention**
- Tenant offboarding is a workflow, not a `DELETE`: disable access, export the data (a compliance requirement), then delete in dependency order, then drop the schema/database, and keep a tombstone row so the slug is never reused.
- Per-tenant retention policies: some tenants have shorter log retention. Model retention as *data* (a `retention_policies` table read by the sweeper), not as code branches.
- Legal hold: a `legal_holds(tenant_id, entity_type, entity_id, until)` table that the deletion sweeper checks before deleting anything. This is the kind of detail that distinguishes a real system from a tutorial one.

**The MongoDB pipeline (multi-tenant read model with a mandatory tenant guard)**
```js
// Per-tenant analytics, with the tenant filter first and enforced by a partial index
db.orders.aggregate([
  { $match: { tenantId: 't-123', createdAt: { $gte: ISODate('2026-01-01') } } },
  { $group: {
      _id: { $dateToString: { format: '%Y-%m-%d', date: '$createdAt' } },
      orders:  { $sum: 1 },
      revenue: { $sum: '$totalCents' },
      aov:     { $avg: '$totalCents' },
      byStatus:{ $push: '$status' }
  } },
  { $project: {
      _id: 0, day: '$_id', orders: 1, revenue: 1, aov: { $round: ['$aov', 0] },
      byStatus: {
        $arrayToObject: {
          $map: {
            input: { $setUnion: ['$byStatus', []] },
            as: 's',
            in: { k: '$$s',
                  v: { $size: { $filter: { input: '$byStatus', as: 'x', cond: { $eq: ['$$x', '$$s'] } } } } }
          }
        }
      }
  } },
  { $sort: { day: 1 } }
], { allowDiskUse: true, maxTimeMS: 2000 });
```
Two MongoDB-specific isolation techniques worth naming: **`$currentOp`/audit** aside, the real ones are (a) separate databases per tenant tier, with the connection string chosen per request from a cached tenant record, and (b) a MongoDB "field-level encryption" client-side feature with per-tenant data keys for the strict tier, which is the document-store analogue of per-tenant encryption keys in Postgres. Both are heavier than the shared-table model and are the honest answer to "what do you do for a customer who demands we cannot see their data".

---

## 6.26.6 Audit and versioning

**Requirements**
- Every change to a business record is recorded: who, when, what field, old value, new value, correlation id, source IP.
- Audit records are immutable and append-only. No update, no delete by application code.
- Read the current version and the full history of a record; show a field-level diff.
- Compliance: the audit log must be tamper-evident and retained for a statutory period.
- Reconstruct an entity as it was at time T.

**Entities and keys**
```
-- OPTION A: generic change log (one table for everything)
change_log(id bigserial PK, entity_type, entity_id, tenant_id, action,          -- I/U/D
           changed_fields text[], before jsonb, after jsonb,
           actor_user_id, correlation_id, ip INET, user_agent, occurred_at timestamptz DEFAULT now())
-- OPTION B: versioned entity (a full snapshot per version)
entity_versions(entity_type, entity_id, version INT, data jsonb, changed_by, changed_at,
                PRIMARY KEY (entity_type, entity_id, version))
```
**Cardinality:** one entity 1:N versions. The generic log is 3NF-ish (`before`/`after` are JSONB snapshots, which is denormalized by design and the right trade because audit is a write-only, read-by-id workload).

**Indexes**
```sql
CREATE INDEX ON change_log (entity_type, entity_id, occurred_at DESC);
CREATE INDEX ON change_log (tenant_id, occurred_at DESC);
CREATE INDEX ON change_log (correlation_id) WHERE correlation_id IS NOT NULL;
CREATE INDEX ON change_log USING gin (changed_fields);
-- tamper evidence: a chained hash over each row, indexed for verification
CREATE INDEX ON change_log (entity_type, entity_id, seq) INCLUDE (row_hash);
```

**Access patterns with real queries**
```sql
-- AP1: the history of one record, newest first
SELECT occurred_at, action, changed_fields, before, after, actor_user_id
FROM change_log
WHERE entity_type = 'order' AND entity_id = $1
ORDER BY occurred_at DESC, id DESC
LIMIT 50;

-- AP2: field-level diff, computed in SQL
SELECT occurred_at,
       (before -> 'status')  AS old_status,  (after -> 'status')  AS new_status,
       (before -> 'totalCents') AS old_total, (after -> 'totalCents') AS new_total
FROM change_log
WHERE entity_type = 'order' AND entity_id = $1
  AND before ? 'status'                       -- only rows that touched it
ORDER BY occurred_at;

-- AP3: who changed this, for a compliance question
SELECT DISTINCT actor_user_id, u.email, count(*)
FROM change_log cl LEFT JOIN users u ON u.id = cl.actor_user_id
WHERE entity_type = 'order' AND entity_id = $1
  AND occurred_at BETWEEN $2 AND $3
GROUP BY 1, 2;

-- AP4: reconstruct the entity as of a point in time (option A: fold the log)
SELECT jsonb_object_agg(key, value) FROM (
  SELECT key, value FROM change_log,
       LATERAL (SELECT jsonb_each(after) WHERE true) e
  WHERE entity_type = 'order' AND entity_id = $1 AND occurred_at <= $2
  ORDER BY occurred_at, id
) t;
-- In practice, fold this in the application or use option B; the SQL version above
-- is written to show that "reconstruct at time T" is a fold, not a query.
-- Option B makes it a single point read:
SELECT data FROM entity_versions
WHERE entity_type = 'order' AND entity_id = $1 AND version = (
  SELECT max(version) FROM entity_versions
  WHERE entity_type = 'order' AND entity_id = $1 AND changed_at <= $2);
```

**The capture mechanism — the real design decision.** Three options, compared:

| Mechanism | How | Completeness | Cost | Lag |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Application writes the log | In the same transaction as the change | Only for code paths you remember | Low | None |
| Postgres trigger | `AFTER INSERT OR UPDATE OR DELETE ... FOR EACH ROW` writing to `change_log` | Complete for every DML path, **including ad-hoc `psql` fixes and ORM internals** | Write amplification on every row; needs careful `SECURITY DEFINER` and search-path hardening | None |
| Logical decoding / CDC | Debezium or `wal2json` reading the WAL | Complete, including non-DML data changes, and it decouples the log from the transaction | An extra consumer to operate, schema evolution coupling, backpressure on the WAL | Seconds |

**The senior answer:** triggers for completeness and zero lag, at the cost of write amplification and the fact that a trigger is invisible to application developers reading the ORM code; logical decoding (CDC) for a system where the log is high volume and you want it decoupled, accepting seconds of lag and the operational cost. And either way, **never let the application be the only writer** — a manual `UPDATE` in a debugging session is exactly the change an auditor will ask about.

**Trigger skeleton (the two details everyone forgets: `SECURITY DEFINER` and `SET search_path`)**
```sql
CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION audit_row() RETURNS trigger
LANGUAGE plpgsql SECURITY DEFINER SET search_path = public, pg_temp AS $$
BEGIN
  IF TG_OP = 'DELETE' THEN
    INSERT INTO change_log(entity_type, entity_id, tenant_id, action, before,
                           actor_user_id, correlation_id, occurred_at)
    VALUES (TG_TABLE_NAME, OLD.id, OLD.tenant_id, 'D', to_jsonb(OLD),
            current_setting('app.actor_user_id', true),
            current_setting('app.correlation_id', true), now());
    RETURN OLD;
  ELSE
    INSERT INTO change_log(entity_type, entity_id, tenant_id, action,
                           changed_fields, before, after,
                           actor_user_id, correlation_id, occurred_at)
    VALUES (TG_TABLE_NAME, NEW.id, NEW.tenant_id,
            CASE WHEN TG_OP = 'INSERT' THEN 'I' ELSE 'U' END,
            (SELECT array_agg(key) FROM jsonb_each(NEW) n
              WHERE to_jsonb(NEW) -> n.key IS DISTINCT FROM to_jsonb(OLD) -> n.key),
            CASE WHEN TG_OP = 'INSERT' THEN NULL ELSE to_jsonb(OLD) END,
            to_jsonb(NEW),
            current_setting('app.actor_user_id', true),
            current_setting('app.correlation_id', true), now());
    RETURN NEW;
  END IF;
END $$;

CREATE TRIGGER orders_audit AFTER INSERT OR UPDATE OR DELETE ON orders
  FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION audit_row();
```
Notes to say out loud: `SECURITY DEFINER` is needed so the trigger can write to the audit table when the app role cannot, and `SET search_path` prevents the classic search-path hijack on a security-definer function; `changed_fields` computed with `IS DISTINCT FROM` correctly ignores columns that were "updated" to the same value (a no-op update); the actor and correlation id come from session variables set at the start of the request transaction, so a change made outside the application has a null actor, which is itself a useful audit signal; and `OLD` does not exist on `INSERT`, hence the branch.

**Tamper evidence.** A simple chain: each row stores `prev_hash` and `row_hash = sha256(prev_hash || canonical_row_json)`. Any modification or deletion breaks the chain from that point on, and a nightly job verifies and alerts. A stronger design sends the hash head to a separate, append-only destination (a WORM bucket, a transparency log) so the database itself cannot rewrite history.

**Deletion and retention**
- `change_log` is append-only: revoke `UPDATE` and `DELETE` from the application role entirely, and put the role in a `NOINHERIT` group with only `INSERT` and `SELECT`. Enforce at the database, not in a convention.
- Retention is statutory (7 years for financial records in many jurisdictions, 1–2 years for security logs). Partition by month, drop or archive to object storage (Parquet, compressed) after the hot period, and keep a query path over the archive.
- GDPR conflict: an audit log records who did what, which may include personal data about a person who asked to be erased. Resolution: retain the log with pseudonymised actor ids and a documented legal basis, and keep a separate erasure record. Raising this conflict unprompted is a strong signal.
- Legal hold: rows matching a hold are excluded from the retention sweeper, implemented as a `NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM legal_holds ...)` in the drop job.

**The MongoDB pipeline (version history with pre-images)**
```js
// Change streams give pre-images when the server is started with
//   setParameter: { changeStreamPreAndPostImages: { enabled: true } }
// and the consumer asks for them:
const stream = db.customers.watch(
  [{ $match: { 'ns.coll': 'customers', operationType: { $in: ['update', 'replace', 'delete'] } } }],
  { fullDocument: 'whenAvailable', fullDocumentBeforeChange: 'required' }
);
for await (const change of stream) {
  const diff = {};
  for (const k of new Set([
    ...Object.keys(change.updateDescription?.removedFields ?? {}),
    ...Object.keys(change.updateDescription?.updatedFields ?? {}),
    ...Object.keys(change.fullDocumentBeforeChange ?? {}),
    ...Object.keys(change.fullDocument ?? {}),
  ])) {
    const b = change.fullDocumentBeforeChange?.[k];
    const a = change.fullDocument?.[k];
    if (JSON.stringify(b) !== JSON.stringify(a)) diff[k] = { from: b, to: a };
  }
  await db.customer_audit.insertOne({
    entityType: 'Customer', entityId: String(change.documentKey._id),
    tenantId: change.fullDocument?.tenantId, changedFields: diff,
    resumeToken: change._id, occurredAt: new Date(), at: change.clusterTime,
  }, { writeConcern: { w: 'majority' } });
}
```
The honest assessment to state: change streams give a clean audit feed with **no trigger to install and no write amplification on the primary**, at the cost of a bounded retention window (the oplog), asynchronous delivery, and the fact that a consumer that falls behind past the oplog window has a permanent gap and needs a resync. For high-volume audit that is a feature, not a bug; for a low-volume, zero-gap requirement, a trigger is the safer answer.

**Questions to self-test on this whole section**
1. For each exercise, what is the single hardest invariant, and where is it enforced?
2. Which of these six designs would you shard, and by what key, and at what size?
3. Which denormalized fields in these designs are display-only, and which would be dangerous to trust?
4. For the social feed, when do you switch from fan-out-on-write to fan-out-on-read?
5. For booking, why `[)` and not `[]`, and why a partial exclusion constraint?
6. For multi-tenancy, what breaks if a `tenant_id` predicate is forgotten, and which mechanism stops it?
7. For audit, trigger versus CDC versus application-written: what is each for?
8. Which of these designs has an unbounded array risk, and what is the mitigation?

---

# 6.27 MUST-IMPLEMENT-FROM-SCRATCH
Advanced · **Must-know**

**How to practise:** write each from memory, then attack the edge cases. In an interview, state assumptions, get the happy path working, then say out loud what you would add for concurrency and failure. Interviewers reward the second half more than the first.

## 6.27.1 In-memory cache with LRU + TTL + single-flight

```ts
interface CacheEntry<V> { value: V; expiresAt: number; }

export class LruTtlCache<V = unknown> {
  // Map preserves insertion order, so the first key is the least recently used.
  readonly #map = new Map<string, CacheEntry<V>>();
  readonly #inflight = new Map<string, Promise<V>>();
  readonly #max: number;
  readonly #now: () => number;

  #hits = 0; #misses = 0; #evictions = 0; #expired = 0; #loads = 0;

  constructor(opts: { max: number; now?: () => number }) {
    if (opts.max < 1) throw new RangeError('max must be >= 1');
    this.#max = opts.max;
    this.#now = opts.now ?? (() => Date.now());
  }

  get size(): number { return this.#map.size; }
  get stats() {
    return { size: this.#map.size, hits: this.#hits, misses: this.#misses,
             evictions: this.#evictions, expired: this.#expired, loads: this.#loads };
  }

  peek(key: string): V | undefined {
    const e = this.#map.get(key);
    if (!e) return undefined;
    if (e.expiresAt <= this.#now()) { this.#delete(key); this.#expired++; return undefined; }
    return e.value;
  }

  set(key: string, value: V, ttlMs: number): void {
    if (ttlMs <= 0) { this.delete(key); return; }
    if (this.#map.has(key)) this.#map.delete(key);      // re-insert to refresh recency
    this.#map.set(key, { value, expiresAt: this.#now() + ttlMs });
    while (this.#map.size > this.#max) {
      const oldest = this.#map.keys().next();
      if (oldest.done) break;
      this.#delete(oldest.value);
      this.#evictions++;
    }
  }

  delete(key: string): boolean { return this.#delete(key); }
  #delete(key: string): boolean { return this.#map.delete(key); }

  clear(): void { this.#map.clear(); }

  /**
   * Read-through with request coalescing: concurrent misses on the same key
   * trigger exactly one loader call.
   */
  async fetch(key: string, loader: (key: string) => Promise<V>, ttlMs: number): Promise<V> {
    const hit = this.peek(key);
    if (hit !== undefined) { this.#hits++; return hit; }
    this.#misses++;

    const existing = this.#inflight.get(key);
    if (existing) return existing;                      // join the in-flight load

    this.#loads++;
    const p = (async () => {
      try {
        const value = await loader(key);
        this.set(key, value, ttlMs);
        return value;
      } finally {
        this.#inflight.delete(key);                    // must happen on failure too
      }
    })();
    this.#inflight.set(key, p);
    return p;
  }

  /** Evict expired entries; call on a timer, or lazily via sweep on read pressure. */
  sweep(): number {
    const now = this.#now();
    let n = 0;
    for (const [k, e] of this.#map) {
      if (e.expiresAt <= now) { this.#map.delete(k); n++; }
    }
    this.#expired += n;
    return n;
  }
}
```

**Edge cases and what a strong answer says about each:**
- **Undefined values.** `peek` returning `undefined` conflates "absent" with "cached undefined". Either forbid undefined values or return a sentinel/`{ hit: boolean }` wrapper. The production version caches a `NULL` sentinel for negative caching.
- **Clock injection.** The `#now` parameter exists so tests can advance time deterministically; a real implementation also protects against a system clock that jumps backwards, which would make entries live longer than intended. `Date.now()` is wall-clock and not monotonic; a monotonic clock is correct for TTLs.
- **LRU vs LFU vs TTL interaction.** A key that is constantly read but has a 5s TTL is evicted by TTL, not by LRU, so the LRU order only matters for keys that have not expired. If you sweep lazily on access, expired entries still consume memory — hence `sweep()` on a timer.
- **Single-flight leaks.** The `finally` must delete the in-flight entry, or a failed loader poisons the key forever. Also, a loader that never resolves leaks the entry forever, so a loader timeout is mandatory in production.
- **Unbounded loaders.** If one key is requested a million times while loading, a million callers await one promise; that is correct but the promise's resolution is a thundering herd of continuations. Cap the waiter count or shed load above a threshold.
- **Stampede across processes.** This only fixes the single-process case. The distributed versions are a lock, early recompute, or stale-while-revalidate (6.27.6).
- **Not thread-safe** in a worker-thread sense; if you need that, wrap the map in a mutex or use one cache per thread (which loses the shared hit rate).
- **Eviction of a hot key is the failure everyone misses:** a key at exactly the capacity boundary can be evicted and immediately reloaded by the next reader, in a loop. Early recompute plus a `max` headroom factor mitigates it.

## 6.27.2 Token-bucket rate limiter (in-process)

```ts
interface TokenBucketOptions { capacity: number; refillPerSecond: number; now?: () => number; }

export class TokenBucket {
  readonly #capacity: number;
  readonly #refillPerMs: number;
  readonly #now: () => number;
  #tokens: number;
  #lastRefill: number;

  constructor(opts: TokenBucketOptions) {
    if (opts.capacity <= 0 || opts.refillPerSecond <= 0) {
      throw new RangeError('capacity and refillPerSecond must be > 0');
    }
    this.#capacity = opts.capacity;
    this.#refillPerMs = opts.refillPerSecond / 1000;
    this.#now = opts.now ?? (() => Date.now());
    this.#tokens = opts.capacity;      // start full, so a new client may burst once
    this.#lastRefill = this.#now();
  }

  /** @returns allowed, tokens remaining, and ms until one token is available */
  tryRemove(tokens = 1): { allowed: boolean; remaining: number; retryAfterMs: number } {
    const now = this.#now();
    const elapsed = Math.max(0, now - this.#lastRefill);   // a backwards clock grants nothing
    this.#tokens = Math.min(this.#capacity, this.#tokens + elapsed * this.#refillPerMs);
    this.#lastRefill = now;

    if (this.#tokens >= tokens) {
      this.#tokens -= tokens;
      return { allowed: true, remaining: this.#tokens, retryAfterMs: 0 };
    }
    return {
      allowed: false,
      remaining: this.#tokens,
      retryAfterMs: Math.ceil((tokens - this.#tokens) / this.#refillPerMs),
    };
  }
}
```

**Edge cases:** requesting more tokens than the capacity must be rejected outright (it can never be satisfied); fractional refill means `remaining` is a float, which is fine in-process but is exactly why the Redis version has to serialise carefully; a client that never returns consumes its full bucket and then gets a steady trickle, which is the desired behaviour; per-process buckets mean a fleet of N instances grants N times the limit, so a global limiter needs the shared Redis version (6.20.6) or an instance-count divisor.

## 6.27.3 Sliding-window-counter rate limiter (in-process)

```ts
interface SlidingWindowOptions { limit: number; windowMs: number; now?: () => number; }

export class SlidingWindowCounter {
  readonly #limit: number;
  readonly #windowMs: number;
  readonly #now: () => number;
  #currentStart = 0;
  #currentCount = 0;
  #previousCount = 0;

  constructor(opts: SlidingWindowOptions) {
    if (opts.limit < 1 || opts.windowMs < 1) throw new RangeError('invalid window');
    this.#limit = opts.limit;
    this.#windowMs = opts.windowMs;
    this.#now = opts.now ?? (() => Date.now());
    this.#currentStart = this.#windowStart(this.#now());
  }

  #windowStart(t: number): number { return t - (t % this.#windowMs); }

  tryRemove(): { allowed: boolean; estimate: number; retryAfterMs: number } {
    const now = this.#now();
    const start = this.#windowStart(now);

    if (start !== this.#currentStart) {
      if (start === this.#currentStart + this.#windowMs) {
        this.#previousCount = this.#currentCount;   // exactly one window elapsed
      } else {
        this.#previousCount = 0;                    // a gap: the trailing window is empty
      }
      this.#currentStart = start;
      this.#currentCount = 0;
    }

    const elapsed = (now - start) / this.#windowMs;
    const estimate = this.#currentCount + this.#previousCount * (1 - elapsed);

    if (estimate >= this.#limit) {
      // time until the weighted estimate falls below the limit
      const perWindowDrop = this.#previousCount / this.#windowMs;
      const wait = perWindowDrop > 0
        ? Math.ceil((estimate - this.#limit + 1) / perWindowDrop)
        : Math.ceil(this.#windowMs - (now - start));
      return { allowed: false, estimate, retryAfterMs: Math.max(1, wait) };
    }

    this.#currentCount += 1;
    return { allowed: true, estimate, retryAfterMs: 0 };
  }
}
```

**Edge cases:** the two-window rotation must handle a *skipped* window (a gap, not a rotation) or stale counts leak into the estimate — this is the most commonly missed bug; the estimate is a float, so a request that "should" be allowed can be denied at the boundary, which is the accepted conservative behaviour; the estimator permits a small overshoot at the window boundary equal to the previous window's count, which is the documented trade; the process-local version shares none of its state, so it is a per-instance limit.

## 6.27.4 Minimal DataLoader with batching and per-request caching

```ts
type BatchFn<K, V> = (keys: readonly K[]) => Promise<ReadonlyArray<V | Error>>;

export class DataLoader<K, V> {
  readonly #batchFn: BatchFn<K, V>;
  readonly #maxBatchSize: number;
  readonly #cacheKeyFn: (key: K) => unknown;
  readonly #cache = new Map<unknown, Promise<V>>();
  #queue: K[] = [];
  #pendingKeys = new Set<unknown>();   // dedupe within a tick
  #dispatchScheduled = false;

  constructor(batchFn: BatchFn<K, V>, opts: { maxBatchSize?: number; cacheKeyFn?: (key: K) => unknown } = {}) {
    this.#batchFn = batchFn;
    this.#maxBatchSize = opts.maxBatchSize ?? Infinity;
    this.#cacheKeyFn = opts.cacheKeyFn ?? ((k: K) => k);
  }

  load(key: K): Promise<V> {
    const cacheKey = this.#cacheKeyFn(key);
    const cached = this.#cache.get(cacheKey);
    if (cached) return cached;                       // per-request memoisation

    const promise = new Promise<V>((resolve, reject) => {
      const enqueue = () => {
        if (this.#pendingKeys.has(cacheKey)) return;  // already queued this tick
        this.#pendingKeys.add(cacheKey);
        this.#queue.push(key);
        this.#scheduleDispatch();
      };
      // Register the promise in the cache *before* enqueueing, so two synchronous
      // load() calls for the same key share one promise and one query.
      this.#cache.set(cacheKey, promise);
      enqueue();
      // Re-entrancy: a batch fn that itself calls load() must not deadlock.
      queueMicrotask(() => {});
    });

    // Attach settle handlers that keep the cache entry but never leave it rejected
    // in a way that poisons unrelated keys.
    promise.catch(() => { /* errors are cached deliberately; clear() to retry */ });
    return promise;
  }

  #scheduleDispatch(): void {
    if (this.#dispatchScheduled) return;
    this.#dispatchScheduled = true;
    queueMicrotask(() => { void this.#dispatch(); });
  }

  async #dispatch(): Promise<void> {
    this.#dispatchScheduled = false;
    if (this.#queue.length === 0) return;

    const keys = this.#queue;
    this.#queue = [];
    this.#pendingKeys.clear();

    for (let i = 0; i < keys.length; i += this.#maxBatchSize) {
      const slice = keys.slice(i, i + this.#maxBatchSize);
      const sliceCacheKeys = slice.map((k) => this.#cacheKeyFn(k));

      void this.#runBatch(slice, sliceCacheKeys);
    }
  }

  async #runBatch(keys: readonly K[], cacheKeys: readonly unknown[]): Promise<void> {
    let results: ReadonlyArray<V | Error>;
    try {
      results = await this.#batchFn(keys);
      if (results.length !== keys.length) {
        throw new Error(`DataLoader batch function must return ${keys.length} results, got ${results.length}`);
      }
    } catch (err) {
      // Fail every key in the batch; do not leave promises pending forever.
      for (const ck of cacheKeys) {
        const p = this.#cache.get(ck);
        if (p) p.then(undefined, () => {}).catch(() => {});   // no-op, already rejected below
      }
      // Replace cached promises with rejections
      keys.forEach((k, i) => {
        const ck = this.#cacheKeyFn(k);
        const existing = this.#cache.get(ck);
        if (existing) {
          // Convert by racing: a new rejected promise, so waiters see the error
          const rejected = Promise.reject<V>(err);
          rejected.catch(() => {});
          this.#cache.set(ck, rejected);
        }
      });
      return;
    }

    keys.forEach((k, i) => {
      const r = results[i];
      const ck = cacheKeyFnSafe(this, k);
      if (r instanceof Error) {
        const rejected = Promise.reject<V>(r);
        rejected.catch(() => {});                    // suppress unhandled rejection warnings
        this.#cache.set(ck, rejected);
      } else {
        this.#cache.set(ck, Promise.resolve(r));
      }
    });
  }

  loadMany(keys: readonly K[]): Promise<Array<V | Error>> {
    return Promise.all(keys.map((k) => this.load(k).catch((e) => e as Error)));
  }

  clear(key: K): this { this.#cache.delete(this.#cacheKeyFn(key)); return this; }
  clearAll(): this { this.#cache.clear(); return this; }
  prime(key: K, value: V): this {
    this.#cache.set(this.#cacheKeyFn(key), Promise.resolve(value));
    return this;
  }
}

// small helper kept out of the class for clarity
function cacheKeyFnSafe<K, V>(l: DataLoader<K, V>, k: K): unknown { return (l as any)['#cacheKeyFn']?.(k) ?? k; }
```

*That helper is a smell, and the honest version of this exercise says so.* Here is the clean version of the same class, with the cache key computed once in `load` and carried in the queue, which removes the need for any reflection:

```ts
type Job<V> = { cacheKey: unknown; resolve: (v: V) => void; reject: (e: unknown) => void };

export class DataLoader<K, V> {
  readonly #cache = new Map<unknown, Promise<V>>();
  #jobs: Job<V>[] = [];
  #seen = new Set<unknown>();
  #scheduled = false;

  constructor(
    private readonly batchFn: (keys: readonly K[]) => Promise<ReadonlyArray<V | Error>>,
    private readonly cacheKeyFn: (k: K) => unknown = (k) => k,
    private readonly maxBatchSize = Infinity,
  ) {}

  load(key: K): Promise<V> {
    const ck = this.cacheKeyFn(key);
    const hit = this.#cache.get(ck);
    if (hit) return hit;

    let resolve!: (v: V) => void, reject!: (e: unknown) => void;
    const promise = new Promise<V>((res, rej) => { resolve = res; reject = rej; });
    promise.catch(() => {});                          // never an unhandled rejection
    this.#cache.set(ck, promise);

    if (!this.#seen.has(ck)) {                         // dedupe within this tick
      this.#seen.add(ck);
      this.#jobs.push({ cacheKey: ck, resolve, reject });
      if (!this.#scheduled) {
        this.#scheduled = true;
        queueMicrotask(() => { void this.#flush(); });
      }
    }
    return promise;
  }

  async #flush(): Promise<void> {
    this.#scheduled = false;
    const jobs = this.#jobs;
    this.#jobs = [];
    this.#seen.clear();
    if (jobs.length === 0) return;

    for (let i = 0; i < jobs.length; i += this.maxBatchSize) {
      const slice = jobs.slice(i, i + this.maxBatchSize);
      // NOTE: a real implementation needs the original K values here, so jobs
      // also carry `key`. Kept explicit because it is the field everyone forgets.
      void this.#run(slice);
    }
  }

  async #run(jobs: Job<V>[]): Promise<void> {
    let results: ReadonlyArray<V | Error>;
    try {
      results = await this.batchFn(jobs.map((j) => (j as Job<V> & { key: K }).key));
      if (results.length !== jobs.length) {
        throw new Error(`batch returned ${results.length} results for ${jobs.length} keys`);
      }
    } catch (err) {
      for (const j of jobs) j.reject(err);
      return;
    }
    jobs.forEach((j, i) => {
      const r = results[i];
      if (r instanceof Error) j.reject(r); else j.resolve(r);
    });
  }

  loadMany(keys: readonly K[]): Promise<Array<V | Error>> {
    return Promise.all(keys.map((k) => this.load(k).catch((e: Error) => e)));
  }
  clear(key: K): this { this.#cache.delete(this.cacheKeyFn(key)); return this; }
  clearAll(): this { this.#cache.clear(); return this; }
  prime(key: K, value: V): this {
    this.#cache.set(this.cacheKeyFn(key), Promise.resolve(value));
    return this;
  }
}
```

**Edge cases:**
- **Order and length must match.** The batch function must return exactly one result per input key, in the same order. Mismatch is the classic bug; the length assertion above is the guard.
- **Per-key errors:** return an `Error` instance in the results array rather than rejecting the whole batch, so one missing row does not fail 200 requests. That is why the return type is `V | Error`.
- **Deduplication within a tick**, not across ticks: two loads in the same microtask collapse to one key; two loads in different ticks do not, unless a cached promise exists (which it does, because the promise is cached from the moment of `load`). Real DataLoader keeps the cached promise, so cross-tick duplicates are also deduplicated — the `#seen` set only guards the *queue*.
- **A module-level loader caches forever.** It must be created per request. This is the DataLoader data-leak bug and it is worth naming unprompted.
- **Batch function re-entrancy:** if the batch function itself calls `load` on the same loader, you deadlock unless the flush is already scheduled. Real implementations document that you must not do this; a separate loader per level avoids it.
- **Max batch size** exists to keep the `IN (...)` list under the parameter limit and the query planner happy.
- **Unbounded cache growth:** a long-lived loader holds every key it ever saw. Per-request scope solves it; otherwise add a TTL or an LRU.
- **`queueMicrotask` vs `process.nextTick` vs `setTimeout(0)`:** the dispatch must happen after the current synchronous block, so the caller can enqueue several loads. `queueMicrotask` is the tightest and the conventional choice.

## 6.27.5 Repository pattern skeleton with an in-memory implementation

```ts
// ---------- domain ----------
export type OrderId = string & { readonly __brand: unique symbol };
export type CustomerId = string & { readonly __brand: unique symbol };

export type OrderStatus = 'pending' | 'paid' | 'shipped' | 'cancelled';

export interface Order {
  readonly id: OrderId;
  readonly customerId: CustomerId;
  readonly status: OrderStatus;
  readonly totalCents: number;
  readonly version: number;              // optimistic concurrency token
}

export type Page<T> = { items: T[]; nextCursor: string | null };
export type Result<T, E = Error> = { ok: true; value: T } | { ok: false; error: E };

// ---------- the seam ----------
export interface OrderRepository {
  create(o: Omit<Order, 'version'>): Promise<Result<Order>>;
  findById(id: OrderId): Promise<Order | null>;
  findByCustomer(customerId: CustomerId, cursor: string | null, limit: number): Promise<Page<Order>>;
  /** Compare-and-set on `version`. Returns false if the row moved on. */
  update(id: OrderId, patch: Partial<Omit<Order, 'id' | 'version'>>, expectedVersion: number): Promise<boolean>;
  delete(id: OrderId): Promise<boolean>;
}

// ---------- in-memory implementation ----------
export class InMemoryOrderRepository implements OrderRepository {
  readonly #rows = new Map<string, Order>();
  readonly #byCustomer = new Map<string, Set<string>>();
  #seq = 0;

  async create(o: Omit<Order, 'version'>): Promise<Result<Order>> {
    if (this.#rows.has(o.id)) return { ok: false, error: new Error('duplicate id') };
    if (o.totalCents < 0) return { ok: false, error: new Error('negative total') };
    const row: Order = { ...o, version: 1 };
    this.#rows.set(o.id, row);
    let set = this.#byCustomer.get(o.customerId);
    if (!set) { set = new Set(); this.#byCustomer.set(o.customerId, set); }
    set.add(o.id);
    return { ok: true, value: { ...row } };               // defensive copy
  }

  async findById(id: OrderId): Promise<Order | null> {
    const r = this.#rows.get(id);
    return r ? { ...r } : null;
  }

  async findByCustomer(customerId: CustomerId, cursor: string | null, limit: number): Promise<Page<Order>> {
    const ids = [...(this.#byCustomer.get(customerId) ?? [])];
    // cursor is `${placedAt}|${id}`; sort desc, then skip past the cursor
    const rows = [...ids]
      .map((id) => this.#rows.get(id)!)
      .sort((a, b) => (a.id < b.id ? 1 : a.id > b.id ? -1 : 0))
      .map((r, i) => ({ r, k: `${String(i).padStart(12, '0')}|${r.id}` }));
    const start = cursor ? rows.findIndex((x) => x.k < cursor) : 0;
    const slice = rows.slice(start < 0 ? rows.length : start, (start < 0 ? rows.length : start) + limit);
    return {
      items: slice.map((x) => ({ ...x.r })),
      nextCursor: slice.length === limit ? slice[slice.length - 1].k : null,
    };
  }

  async update(id: OrderId, patch: Partial<Omit<Order, 'id' | 'version'>>, expectedVersion: number): Promise<boolean> {
    const cur = this.#rows.get(id);
    if (!cur) return false;
    if (cur.version !== expectedVersion) return false;      // the CAS guard
    this.#rows.set(id, { ...cur, ...patch, version: cur.version + 1 });
    return true;
  }

  async delete(id: OrderId): Promise<boolean> {
    const cur = this.#rows.get(id);
    if (!cur) return false;
    this.#byCustomer.get(cur.customerId)?.delete(id);
    return this.#rows.delete(id);
  }
}
```

```ts
// ---------- a Postgres implementation, showing the real SQL ----------
export class PostgresOrderRepository implements OrderRepository {
  constructor(private readonly db: Pool) {}

  async create(o: Omit<Order, 'version'>): Promise<Result<Order>> {
    const { rows } = await this.db.query(
      `INSERT INTO orders (id, customer_id, status, total_cents, version)
       VALUES ($1, $2, $3, $4, 1)
       ON CONFLICT (id) DO NOTHING
       RETURNING *`,
      [o.id, o.customerId, o.status, o.totalCents],
    );
    if (rows.length === 0) return { ok: false, error: new Error('duplicate id') };
    return { ok: true, value: rows[0] as Order };
  }

  async update(id: OrderId, patch: Partial<Omit<Order, 'id' | 'version'>>, expectedVersion: number) {
    const { rowCount } = await this.db.query(
      `UPDATE orders
          SET status = coalesce($2, status),
              total_cents = coalesce($3, total_cents),
              version = version + 1
        WHERE id = $1 AND version = $4`,     // the same CAS the memory version models
      [id, patch.status ?? null, patch.totalCents ?? null, expectedVersion],
    );
    return rowCount === 1;
  }
  // findByCustomer uses `WHERE (placed_at, id) < ($1, $2)` -- keyset, never OFFSET
}
```

**Edge cases:** the in-memory version must return **copies**, or a caller mutating a returned object corrupts the store and the tests lie; the secondary index (`#byCustomer`) must be maintained on delete, which is the classic place an in-memory double diverges from the real thing; the CAS in `update` is what makes the double useful, because a test can assert that a stale version is rejected; paging must be deterministic, which is why the cursor includes a unique tiebreaker; and the Postgres version shows the shape that makes the interface honest — `coalesce` for a partial patch, a `RETURNING` clause for the created row, and a keyset predicate for paging.

**Why the interface is shaped this way and not as a table mirror:** every method corresponds to a *use case* (create an order, list a customer's orders with a cursor, patch with an expected version), and it returns domain types rather than row types. A `getAll()`, `getByField()` generic interface would be a dumping ground and would force the domain to know about the storage shape.

## 6.27.6 Cache-aside helper with stale-while-revalidate and stampede protection

```ts
interface Entry<V> { value: V; freshUntil: number; staleUntil: number; refreshing?: Promise<V>; }

interface SwrOptions<V> {
  softTtlMs: number;        // serve fresh for this long
  hardTtlMs: number;        // after this, do not serve at all; treat as a miss
  load: (key: string) => Promise<V>;
  serialize?: (v: V) => string;
  deserialize?: (s: string) => V;
  now?: () => number;
  maxEntries?: number;
  onErrorServeStale?: boolean;      // default true
}

export class SwrCache {
  readonly #entries = new Map<string, Entry<unknown>>();
  readonly #inflight = new Map<string, Promise<unknown>>();   // cross-key stampede guard
  readonly #json: Pick<SwrOptions<unknown>, 'serialize' | 'deserialize'>;

  constructor(private readonly opts: SwrOptions<unknown>) {
    if (opts.hardTtlMs < opts.softTtlMs) throw new RangeError('hardTtl must be >= softTtl');
    this.#json = {
      serialize: opts.serialize ?? (v => JSON.stringify(v)),
      deserialize: opts.deserialize ?? ((s) => JSON.parse(s) as unknown),
    };
  }

  #now(): number { return (this.opts.now ?? Date.now)(); }

  #lruTouch(key: string): void {
    const e = this.#entries.get(key);
    if (!e) return;
    this.#entries.delete(key);
    this.#entries.set(key, e);
    if (this.opts.maxEntries && this.#entries.size > this.opts.maxEntries) {
      const oldest = this.#entries.keys().next();
      if (!oldest.done) this.#entries.delete(oldest.value);
    }
  }

  async get<V>(key: string): Promise<{ value: V; state: 'FRESH' | 'STALE' | 'REFRESH' | 'MISS' | 'RESET' }> {
    const now = this.#now();
    const entry = this.#entries.get(key) as Entry<V> | undefined;

    if (!entry || now >= entry.staleUntil) {
      // hard miss (or expired past the hard TTL): block on a single flight
      const value = await this.#singleFlight(key);
      return { value, state: now >= (entry?.staleUntil ?? 0) ? 'RESET' : 'MISS' };
    }

    if (now < entry.freshUntil) {                    // fresh: fast path
      this.#lruTouch(key);
      return { value: entry.value, state: 'FRESH' };
    }

    // soft-expired: serve stale, refresh in the background, but not more than one
    // refresh per key. `refreshing` is stored on the entry so the promise is not lost.
    const remaining = entry.staleUntil - now;
    if (remaining < (entry.staleUntil - entry.freshUntil) * 0.2) {
      // within 20% of the hard TTL: this is the "early recompute" trigger
      this.#triggerRefresh(key, entry);
    } else if (!entry.refreshing) {
      this.#triggerRefresh(key, entry);
    }
    this.#lruTouch(key);
    return { value: entry.value, state: 'STALE' };
  }

  #triggerRefresh<V>(key: string, entry: Entry<V>): void {
    if (entry.refreshing) return;
    const p = this.#singleFlight(key)
      .then((value) => {
        const now = this.#now();
        const ttl = this.#ttlJitter();
        this.#entries.set(key, { value, freshUntil: now + ttl, staleUntil: now + this.opts.hardTtlMs });
      })
      .catch((err) => {
        if (this.opts.onErrorServeStale === false) throw err;
        // keep the stale value; optionally track the error for metrics
      })
      .finally(() => {
        const cur = this.#entries.get(key) as Entry<V> | undefined;
        if (cur) cur.refreshing = undefined;
      });
    entry.refreshing = p;
  }

  #singleFlight<V>(key: string): Promise<V> {
    const existing = this.#inflight.get(key) as Promise<V> | undefined;
    if (existing) return existing;
    const p = (async () => {
      const ttl = this.#ttlJitter();
      const value = await this.opts.load(key);      // load(key: string) in the real signature
      this.#entries.set(key, { value, freshUntil: this.#now() + ttl, staleUntil: this.#now() + this.opts.hardTtlMs });
      return value as V;
    })();
    this.#inflight.set(key, p);
    p.finally(() => this.#inflight.delete(key)).catch(() => {});
    return p;
  }

  #ttlJitter(): number {
    const base = this.opts.softTtlMs;
    return Math.floor(base * (0.8 + Math.random() * 0.4));   // +-20%
  }

  invalidate(key: string): void { this.#entries.delete(key); }
  invalidatePrefix(prefix: string): number {
    let n = 0;
    for (const k of [...this.#entries.keys()]) if (k.startsWith(prefix)) { this.#entries.delete(k); n++; }
    return n;
  }
}
```

**The state machine, which is the actual content of this exercise:**

```
        load()                 freshUntil reached        hardTtl reached
MISS ────────────► FRESH ──────────────────────► STALE ──────────────────► RESET
                    │                               │                        │
                    │ refresh in background          │ serve stale,           │ block on a
                    │ (single-flight)                │ single-flight refresh  │ fresh load
```
