```
                    ▼                               ▼                        ▼
                 FRESH                          FRESH (or stays STALE
                                                    if the refresh failed)
```
- **The three TTLs, not two.** `softTtl` (freshness), `hardTtl` (staleness ceiling), and the refresh timeout. Setting only one TTL means either serving stale data forever after a failed refresh (bad) or never being fresh (pointless).
- **Single-flight is the stampede protection**, and it is what makes the STALE path safe: 1,000 concurrent readers of a stale key trigger one load, and the other 999 get the stale value *immediately* rather than waiting. That is the whole point of SWR: the request is never blocked after the first fill.
- **The `#inflight` map is separate from `entry.refreshing` on purpose.** `#inflight` guards *all* concurrent loads including hard misses; `entry.refreshing` guards the *background* refresh so a stale key does not spawn a refresh per reader. Confusing the two is the most common bug in hand-rolled SWR.
- **A failed refresh must not delete the entry.** Serving slightly-stale data beats serving a 500. That is the `onErrorServeStale` behaviour, and a circuit breaker around `load` is the production addition.
- **Early recompute** (refresh inside the last 20% of the stale window) removes the synchronized-expiry wave that plain TTL creates. Combined with `#ttlJitter`, it is the difference between a smooth miss rate and a periodic cliff.
- **Negative caching** needs a sentinel value, not `null`, or `null` results are reloaded forever.
- **The distributed version** replaces `#inflight` with a Redis lock plus a short sleep-and-retry, and the state must live in Redis (or the entry is per-process and the SWR benefit disappears across a fleet).

**Questions**
1. Why is LRU implemented with a `Map` and not a linked list here, and when would you need the list?
2. How do you distinguish "cached undefined" from "not cached"?
3. Why must the single-flight `finally` run on failure too?
4. What breaks if the loader never resolves?
5. How does an early-recompute trigger differ from a background refresh, and why do both?
6. In a token bucket, what happens if a caller requests more tokens than the capacity?
7. What happens to a token bucket if the system clock jumps backwards?
8. In a sliding window counter, what is the difference between a rotation and a gap, and what is the bug if you confuse them?
9. Why does the sliding window estimator permit a small overshoot?
10. What must a DataLoader's batch function guarantee about result order and length?
11. How do you return a per-key error from a batch without failing the whole batch?
12. Why must a DataLoader be request-scoped, and what is the failure mode if it is not?
13. What is the deadlock hazard if a batch function calls `load` on its own loader?
14. Why does the in-memory repository return copies?
15. What is the point of the `expectedVersion` parameter in the repository interface?
16. Why must a cursor include a unique tiebreaker?
17. Why must `hardTtlMs >= softTtlMs`, and what is the consequence of the opposite?
18. Why keep `#inflight` separate from `entry.refreshing`?
19. What should happen when the background refresh fails?
20. What is the Redis equivalent of `#inflight`, and what does it not fix?

---

# 6.28 Data and Database Interview Q&A
Advanced · **Must-know**

| # | Question | Strong answer outline |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | When would you *not* use a relational database? | When the dominant workload is a bounded set of key or prefix lookups over a very wide keyspace at extreme ingest, when the read unit and write unit are the same deeply nested document, or when the data is a time-series with per-series retention. Even then, first check whether Postgres with JSONB, partitioning, or an extension covers it — one engine is usually the right answer and every additional engine is an operational cost |
| 2 | What is the single biggest mistake in NoSQL adoption? | Migrating because the data is "unstructured" without enumerating the access patterns. NoSQL stores trade query flexibility for write and read performance on *known* queries; an un-enumerated access pattern becomes an `ALLOW FILTERING`, a `Scan`, or an N+1 |
| 3 | Explain CAP honestly. | Partition tolerance is not a choice for a distributed system; during a partition you choose between availability and per-operation consistency, and C is scoped to an operation, not a database. The practical axis is latency versus consistency (PACELC) |
| 4 | What is linearizable consistency and what does it cost? | A total order where a read after a completed write sees it or something later. Costs: a round trip to a leader or a quorum, and roughly double the read cost in DynamoDB. It is unavailable on GSIs and cannot be used inside a MongoDB transaction |
| 5 | Sequential vs causal vs read-your-writes? | Sequential = all nodes apply writes in one order but reads may be stale. Causal = reads that have seen A also see B (MongoDB causal sessions, Cassandra session timestamps). Read-your-writes is the weakest and the most commonly needed: the writer never sees stale data |
| 6 | What is a failure detector, and why is a fixed timeout insufficient? | Failure cannot be observed directly, only inferred from silence. A fixed timeout either causes false evictions (availability blips) or slow detection (longer outage). Phi accrual detectors track a distribution of heartbeat intervals and adapt to GC pauses and jitter |
| 7 | Why does a distributed lock need a fencing token? | Because a failure detector can be wrong: a paused process can resume after its lease expired and act on stale authority. A monotonically increasing token checked by the resource makes an old holder's write rejectable. Redlock has this weakness, which is why the Redis project recommends a single instance with `SET NX PX` plus a token for efficiency locks, and a consensus system plus fencing for correctness locks |
| 8 | When are Postgres advisory locks better than a Redis lock? | Whenever you already run Postgres: the lock is transactional, auto-released on commit/rollback (with the `xact` variant) or on connection close, and it cannot be leaked by a crashed process. Use `pg_try_advisory_lock` or `pg_advisory_xact_lock`. Careful with session-level locks on pooled connections |
| 9 | How do you prevent a cache stampede? | Single-flight in-process, a distributed lock with a token, or early recompute inside the stale window. Best in practice: stale-while-revalidate so no reader blocks after the first fill, plus TTL jitter so keys do not expire together |
| 10 | Cache-aside vs write-through? | Cache-aside keeps the store authoritative and the cache disposable, at the cost of a possible miss on read and an invalidation obligation. Write-through has no staleness window but introduces a dual write that can fail or diverge, and makes the cache non-disposable |
| 11 | When is a cache entry a correctness problem rather than a performance one? | When a read can authorize an action (permissions, entitlements, pricing, fraud signals, feature gating for a paid feature), or when a stale value is irreversible (an emailed invoice, a shipped label). Then you need event-driven invalidation or versioned keys, not a TTL |
| 12 | Why TTL jitter? | Fixed TTLs loaded together expire together, producing a periodic miss cliff. Jitter of ±20% spreads expiry and lowers peak miss rate substantially for a one-line change |
| 13 | What is `maxmemory` eviction and why is it scary? | When Redis hits its memory cap it starts deleting keys per the policy, which is silent data loss for anything not rebuildable. Keep caches on `allkeys-lru` and authoritative data on `noeviction`, on separate instances, so one class cannot evict the other |
| 14 | Fixed window vs sliding window vs token bucket? | Fixed: cheapest, 2x burst at boundaries. Sliding log: exact, memory proportional to the limit. Sliding counter: near-exact, two counters. Token bucket: continuous refill, allows a defined burst. Leaky bucket: smooths the *output* rate for a fragile downstream |
| 15 | Why is a rate limiter a separate Redis instance? | The limiter's keyspace must not be evictable (`noeviction`) and its growth must not be able to flush the cache. Sharing an instance couples two unrelated failure modes, and an eviction storm in the cache would hand every client a fresh quota |
| 16 | Rate limiting across regions? | Either a home-region limiter (extra cross-region latency, a hole if the region is down) or a per-region limiter with the limit divided by region count. State which one and the resulting global limit explicitly |
| 17 | Fail open or fail closed? | A decision per endpoint, made deliberately: fail closed for authentication, payments, and anything that would be abused; fail open for reads, with metrics and alerting. It must be a config flag that is alerted on, because a stuck fail-open flag during an incident is expensive |
| 18 | MongoDB embed vs reference? | Embed when the child is bounded, read only with the parent, and updated with it. Reference when it grows unboundedly, is queried or paged independently, or updates at a different rate. The unbounded-growth rule is the shortcut: anything that grows with time or activity must not be embedded |
| 19 | What is the ESR rule? | Equality fields, then Sort, then Range. The sort goes before the range because an in-memory sort is an immediate 100MB-limit failure while a wider range scan is merely slower. Put equality first so the index is reusable for any subset of the equalities |
| 20 | What is a multikey index limitation? | An index can contain at most one array field, because two arrays would require parallel array entries. Compound indexes with two array fields are rejected |
| 21 | Partial vs sparse vs TTL indexes? | Partial: only documents matching an expression, and the query must be a superset of it. Sparse: only documents where the field is absent — note that `null` counts as present, so partial is the better soft-delete pattern. TTL: deletion after a deadline, by a background thread, therefore approximate |
| 22 | How do you retire an index safely? | Mark it `hidden: true` (still maintained on write, invisible to the planner), watch the metrics, then drop it. Dropping a live index makes queries that depended on it fall back to a collection scan |
| 23 | What is the 100MB sort limit? | Blocking sorts and large pipeline stages refuse to exceed 100MB of in-memory working set. Fixes: narrow the query, provide the sort with an index, or set `allowDiskUse: true` and accept disk I/O |
| 24 | When do you need a MongoDB transaction? | When two documents must change atomically and no cheaper boundary exists. Before using one, try to reshape the data so the unit of change is a single document. Transactions require a replica set, are slow, and are bounded by the oplog window |
| 25 | What are change streams and what are their limits? | An oplog-backed cursor with resume tokens. At-least-once, no ordering across collections, bounded by the oplog window (a consumer that falls too far behind must resync), invalidated by a collection drop, and not a durable queue |
| 26 | `w: 1` versus `w: "majority"`? | `w: 1` can be rolled back by a failover, so an acknowledged write can be lost. `w: "majority"` survives a primary change. Combined with a proper election timeout, majority is what you want for anything you cannot reconstruct |
| 27 | What is a scatter-gather and what does it cost? | A query that omits the shard key fans out to all shards; each returns up to `limit` rows, `mongos` performs a blocking merge sort, and latency is the slowest shard. `limit: 10` on 20 shards can pull 200 documents and the 100MB sort limit applies |
| 28 | What must a DynamoDB shard key satisfy? | Immutable, never missing, always indexed, high cardinality and even distribution, and matching the dominant query. Also: a unique index must be prefixed by the shard key, so a bare unique `_id` is only unique per shard |
| 29 | Hot partitions? | One key at 1,000 WCU/1,000 RCU per second caps your table. Fix by moving a time or sequence component into the sort key, write-sharding with a random prefix, DAX for read-heavy skew, or fanning a read across replicated copies |
| 30 | LSI vs GSI? | An LSI shares the base table's capacity (no extra write cost, same key schema prefix) and only supports eventually consistent reads. A GSI is a separate structure with its own read and write capacity and its own eventual consistency. Use sparse GSIs so the index only contains the subset that belongs in it |
| 31 | `ConsistentRead: false` and a missing item? | An eventually consistent read immediately after a write can return nothing at all for that key. Retry; do not conclude the write failed. Strongly consistent reads cost 2x and are not allowed on GSIs |
| 32 | Conditional writes? | `attribute_not_exists(pk)` for create-if-absent and a version or value comparison for compare-and-swap. This is the only way to prevent a lost update in a store with no transactions across items. `ReturnValuesOnConditionCheckFailure: ALL_OLD` lets you report the delta |
| 33 | DynamoDB on-demand versus provisioned? | On-demand is roughly 2x per unit but instant and no-throttle; you pay a double rate for sudden spikes beyond twice the trailing peak. Provisioned with auto scaling is cheaper at steady state and can throttle while scaling reacts |
| 34 | DynamoDB Streams and the two-copies problem? | The table and the stream are both views of the truth. Consumers must be idempotent, dedupe by event id, and handle the case where the stream lags the table (read the table for the latest state, then apply the event) |
| 35 | Why Cassandra needs one table per query? | No joins, no ad-hoc indexes, no cross-partition predicates. Each query shape gets its own table or materialized view, which multiplies storage and write cost and makes schema evolution a real project |
| 36 | `ALLOW FILTERING`? | It reads the whole partition and filters in memory, so cost is proportional to partition size and unbounded memory is possible. A debugging tool, and a scheduled outage in production |
| 37 | The `R + W > N` rule? | Reads plus writes must exceed the replication factor for the read and write replica sets to overlap, which is what prevents a read missing an acknowledged write. With `N=3`, `W=2, R=2` guarantees it; `W=1, R=1` does not |
| 38 | Hinted handoff and `gc_grace`? | A coordinator stores a hint for a down replica and delivers it on return. After `gc_grace` (default 10 days) hints and tombstones are dropped, so a long outage requires `nodetool repair` or a rebuild. Plan node downtime against your TTL, because a node is `UNREACHABLE` for twice the TTL after a long outage |
| 39 | When is Cassandra right? | Very high write ingest, a small known set of queries stated in advance, bounded partition sizes, time-ordered data with retention, multi-DC with local reads. Wrong for ad-hoc work, reporting, cross-entity transactions, or a small dataset |
| 40 | When is a graph database genuinely right? | When traversal depth is unbounded and the relationship carries data: fraud rings, blast radius, multi-hop recommendations, org charts. A one- or two-hop join is faster, transactional, and better documented in a relational store |
| 41 | How do you model a hyperedge? | As an intermediate node, because a property-graph relationship connects exactly two nodes. The same applies in a relational schema: an association table whose key includes the set members |
| 42 | What is a time-series cardinality trap? | Prometheus series count is the product of targets and label value combinations, so one high-cardinality label (`user_id`, `request_id`, `error_message`, a URL with an id) multiplies memory and can OOM the TSDB. High-cardinality values belong in fields, not labels |
| 43 | HNSW or IVFFlat? | HNSW: higher recall, higher memory, slow build, tunable with `ef_search`, best for read-heavy at up to low millions. IVFFlat: fast build, lower recall, needs representative training data, tunable with `probes`, better for large batch ingests on memory-constrained nodes |
| 44 | Why does filtered vector search lose recall? | The ANN index returns the globally nearest neighbours and the filter is applied afterwards, so a selective filter can leave you with almost no results. Fix with `hnsw.iterative_scan`, over-fetching, per-tenant partitions, or an engine with native pre-filtering |
| 45 | When is pgvector enough? | Up to a few million vectors, modest QPS, embeddings that must join transactionally to relational data, and hybrid search with `tsvector`. Graduate on volume, QPS, pre-filtered recall at high recall, or multi-region replicas |
| 46 | N+1 in an ORM: what causes it? | Lazy relations resolved per row: `include` without a join strategy, `populate`, or `relations` in a loop. Detect with query counts in tests, APM spans grouped by statement shape, and `pg_stat_statements` showing a cheap statement thousands of times per second |
| 47 | Fixes for N+1? | DataLoader batching with a request-scoped cache, eager loading as a SQL join, split queries (`WHERE id IN (...)` then stitch), or raw SQL. A module-level DataLoader leaks data between requests and is the classic version of this bug |
| 48 | What is the ORM interactive-transaction pitfall? | The transaction pins one connection, so any reference to the global client inside the callback escapes the transaction and commits partially. Also: `Promise.all` gives no parallelism there, and the default 5s timeout punishes any network call |
| 49 | Expand/contract migrations? | Four separate deploys: add (nullable), backfill in batches, switch reads, drop later. Doing them together makes the first deploy slow and destroys the rollback path. On Postgres, use `NOT VALID` + `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` for foreign keys and `NOT NULL`, and `CONCURRENTLY` for indexes |
| 50 | Which DDL blocks writes? | `ADD COLUMN` with a volatile default, `ALTER COLUMN TYPE` (rewrites), `SET NOT NULL` (verifying scan, unless a validated check constraint exists), `ADD FOREIGN KEY` (locks both tables), and a plain `CREATE INDEX` (blocks writes). `lock_timeout` plus retry turns a table-wide stall into a bounded failure |
| 51 | Raw SQL or ORM? | The ORM is the compiler for CRUD-shaped queries; raw SQL for window functions, CTEs, `LATERAL`, `DISTINCT ON`, locks, and hints. Keep the schema and migrations in the ORM so there is one source of truth, and never concatenate an identifier from user input |
| 52 | When is a repository pattern worth it? | When unit tests genuinely benefit from a no-database double, when the domain must stay free of the ORM, or when two backends coexist during a migration. When it is one backend and a thin CRUD surface, it is indirection with a mapping tax |
| 53 | Elasticsearch near-real-time search? | Writes are not searchable until the next refresh (default 1s). `?refresh=wait_for` for read-your-write flows, `?refresh=true` only in tests. A smaller refresh interval means more segments, more heap, and more merge work |
| 54 | `keyword` versus `text`? | `keyword` for exact matching, sorting, and aggregation (stored whole, unanalyzed, with an `ignore_above` that silently stops indexing long values); `text` for full-text, analyzed, not aggregatable. A multi-field gives you both |
| 55 | Mapping explosion? | Every new field name becomes a cluster-state entry, capped by `total_fields.limit`. Fix with `dynamic: strict`, dynamic templates, the `flattened` type, or a separate index per entity type |
| 56 | `object` versus `nested`? | `object` flattens, losing the association between array element fields; `nested` gives each element its own Lucene document (correct, slower, more index). Use `nested` only when you must query array elements independently |
| 57 | Deep pagination? | `from` + `size` is capped at 10,000 and materializes skipped hits. Use a PIT plus `search_after` with a stable, unique sort tuple including `_shard_doc` |
| 58 | BM25 and its knobs? | `k1` controls term-frequency saturation, `b` controls document-length normalisation. Defaults (1.2, 0.75) are fine; most relevance problems are analyzer and field-structure problems, not parameter problems |
| 59 | Synonyms? | Changing a synonym filter changes the analysis of the whole index, so it is a reindex or a search-time-only `synonym_graph` set. Test them and expect relevance to shift |
| 60 | Fact table grain? | "One row per order line per day." If you cannot state the grain in one sentence, aggregations will disagree, and late or restated facts make it worse. Partition the fact table by date and restate only closed periods |
| 61 | ETL or ELT? | ELT won: land raw, transform in the warehouse where compute and storage are cheap, and keep raw for reprocessing. Batch for stable upstreams, micro-batch for near-real-time dashboards, streaming or CDC for low latency |
| 62 | Exactly once? | Not achievable end to end. Build at-least-once delivery plus an idempotent sink: stable event ids, dedupe windows, or a merge/upsert on a natural key, plus checkpointing after side effects are durable |
| 63 | Late-arriving data? | Window on event time with a watermark and an allowed lateness, route older events to a late-arrival path, and recompute affected closed windows. Processing-time windows put a 5-minute-late event in the wrong bucket and your totals never reconcile |
| 64 | Multipart upload cost leak? | An aborted multipart upload is billed forever. `AbortIncompleteMultipartUpload` in the lifecycle configuration is the single most valuable line there. Also: the multipart ETag is not an MD5 |
| 65 | When is Postgres FTS the right search answer? | A few million documents, exact prefix matching via `pg_trgm`, and filters — with the transactional benefit of the data being in the same database. Move to Elasticsearch for analyzers, phrase/proximity tuning, faceting at scale, and multilingual relevance |
| 66 | Tenancy isolation: what actually stops a leak? | `tenant_id NOT NULL` everywhere, the tenant as the leading index column, uniqueness scoped per tenant, cache keys prefixed by tenant, and row-level security as the safety net for the forgotten predicate. Application-level filters alone are a discipline problem, not a control |
| 67 | How do you make an audit log trustworthy? | Enforce immutability with database privileges (no UPDATE/DELETE for the app role), capture changes with triggers or CDC rather than application code, add a hash chain for tamper evidence, and set a legal-hold exemption in the retention sweeper |
| 68 | Trigger versus CDC for audit? | Triggers: complete, zero lag, invisible to developers, write amplification. CDC: no trigger, no write amplification, decoupled, but seconds of lag, an oplog retention bound, and a resync path to operate |
| 69 | Object storage and rename? | There is no rename. `CopyObject` + `Delete` is a full billed server-side copy and is not atomic, so the pattern is immutable objects plus a mutable pointer in a database, deleted later |
| 70 | What is the most common way a "scalable" NoSQL design fails? | The shard key is chosen for write distribution while the dominant read omits it, so 90% of traffic becomes a scatter-gather. Design the shard key from the access patterns, and confirm with `EXPLAIN` that queries are targeted |

---

# 6.29 Debugging Scenarios
Advanced · **Must-know**

**Format for each scenario:** Symptoms → Possible causes → Investigation → Tools → Fix → Prevention. Being able to run this sequence under pressure is the difference between a mid-level and a senior profile.

## 6.29.1 Cache stampede: latency spike and database saturation on a hot key

**Symptoms.** A single endpoint's p99 goes from 40ms to 4s. Database CPU pins at 100%. Queries are identical point reads on one key. The incident coincides with a scheduled job or a traffic peak, not with a deploy.

**Possible causes.**
- A popular key's TTL expired and thousands of concurrent requests all missed simultaneously.
- A cache flush or a Redis failover emptied the cache (failover loses in-flight writes; a restart with an empty RDB starts cold).
- A `maxmemory` eviction sweep removed the hot key, and it never got back in because every request missed.
- A single-flight guard exists per process but the fleet is 50 instances, so 50 loads still hit the database — the fix was applied but only helped locally.
- A distributed lock exists but the lock TTL (5s) is shorter than the load time (30s), so the lock expires mid-load and everyone piles in behind it.

**Investigation.** Confirm the shape: is the load a *single* key or many? `GET` the hot key and check its TTL. Look for a synchronized miss pattern in the hit-rate time series (a cliff, not a slope). Check whether a Redis failover or restart happened in the window. Compare the number of identical database queries per second to the number of app instances — if it is roughly equal to the instance count, the lock is missing or per-process only.

**Tools.** Redis `INFO stats` (`keyspace_hits`, `keyspace_misses`, `expired_keys`), `MONITOR` (carefully, it slows the server) or `SLOWLOG GET`, `OBJECT ENCODING`/`TTL`, `LATENCY DOCTOR`; application hit-rate and single-flight counters; APM traces showing N identical spans; `pg_stat_activity` and `pg_stat_statements`; a dashboard of "loads in flight per key".

**Fix.** Layer them: (1) raise or remove the TTL on the hot key and prefer an event-driven invalidation plus a long TTL; (2) add jitter to the TTL; (3) add distributed single-flight (a Redis lock with a token, TTL longer than the worst expected load, plus a bounded wait then a stale read); (4) serve stale while revalidating so no reader blocks; (5) pre-warm the top-N keys on deploy; (6) as a circuit breaker, cap concurrency on the loader and shed load with a 503 plus `Retry-After` rather than queueing.

**Prevention.** Hit-rate and miss-latency alerts per key space; a "cache cold start" runbook; a synthetic check that reads the top 20 keys every minute; load tests that include a mass-expiry scenario (fill the cache, then wait for TTL); and a rule that no cache entry may have a TTL without jitter.

## 6.29.2 Stale cache: users report old content

**Symptoms.** A campaign page shows yesterday's price. An edit to a product takes minutes to appear. Intermittent, correlates with deploys and with writes from a different service.

**Possible causes.** Cache-aside with no invalidation, and a TTL longer than the staleness tolerance. Invalidations published to Redis Pub/Sub where a subscriber was down (fire-and-forget, no redelivery). A dual-write where the cache write failed and the request still returned 200. Versioned keys where the reader is using a stale version token. A replicated Redis with the read going to a replica that has not received the invalidation yet. A CDN layer in front of the origin that is caching a response that was supposed to be uncacheable (`Cache-Control` misconfigured).

**Investigation.** Reproduce: write via service A, read via service B, measure the propagation delay. Check that the invalidation event is actually consumed (add a counter on the consumer). Check `Cache-Control`, `Age`, and `X-Cache` headers on the response. Confirm whether reads are hitting a replica (`INFO replication` `slave0` lag). Look for a 200 response where the cache write returned an error.

**Tools.** `redis-cli MONITOR` to see the `DEL`/`SET`; consumer lag metrics on the invalidation queue; CDN response headers and purge APIs; `Cache-Control` assertions in tests; a "write then read own write" integration test that runs on every deploy.

**Fix.** Decide what actually must be fresh. For correctness-critical reads, drop the cache on that path. For the rest: write-through on the write path (accepting the dual-write risk with a retry/outbox), or event-driven invalidation through a durable broker with the cache TTL as a backstop, or versioned keys where the writer bumps a version in the store and the reader passes it. Fix the Pub/Sub dependency by moving invalidation to a stream or a queue, and fix the 200-on-failed-cache-write by logging and alerting on it.

**Prevention.** Document the freshness contract for every cached field in a table (field, TTL, invalidation trigger, acceptable staleness). Add a "publish, do not publish" decision per field. Test read-your-writes for every write endpoint. Never rely on Pub/Sub alone.

## 6.29.3 Hot shard: one MongoDB shard is saturated

**Symptoms.** p99 for writes and targeted reads climbs. One shard shows 10x the CPU, disk I/O, and lock wait of the others. `db.currentOp` is full of operations against the same collection. Chunk distribution is heavily skewed.

**Possible causes.** A range shard key on a monotonic value (`createdAt`, an auto-increment id, an ObjectId) so all new writes land in the newest chunk on one shard. A single hot document (a counter, a config, a "current campaign" row) receiving all writes. A shard key with low cardinality so one partition holds most of the data. A very large document rewritten constantly (write amplification, single-document lock contention). The balancer disabled or wedged, so chunks are not moving.

**Investigation.** `sh.status()` and `db.collection.getShardDistribution()` for chunk skew. `db.currentOp({ active: true })` and the slow-query log for the offending statements and their shard. `db.collection.stats()` for data size distribution and average object size. `db.serverStatus().shardingStatistics` for balancer activity. Check the shard key's value distribution on the primary: `db.collection.aggregate([{ $group: { _id: '$shardKey', n: { $sum: 1 } } }, { $sort: { n: -1 } }])`.

**Tools.** `mongos` logs and the slow-query log (`setParameter logLevel`), Atlas performance advisor, the `sh` shell helpers, `currentOp` and `db.setProfilingLevel(2)`, per-shard Prometheus exporters.

**Fix.** Change the shard key (reshard, which is an online but heavy operation) to a hashed or compound hashed key, or add a bucketing/random component to the key so writes spread. For a single hot document, split it (per-user counters, per-shard counters merged by a job) or move the counter to Redis and flush periodically. Turn the balancer back on and watch it, or use zone sharding to place the hot range deliberately. Reduce document size for the hot collection.

**Prevention.** Load-test with a realistic key distribution, not uniform data. Alert on per-shard CPU and on the max-to-median data-size ratio. Track "hot key" cardinality in a design review. Document the shard key and the reason for it next to the collection definition.

## 6.29.4 Redis eviction storm: keys vanishing, sessions logging users out

**Symptoms.** A wave of 500s and cache misses; users are logged out at random; rate limiters start letting everything through; `OOM command not allowed` errors appear; a batch job fails with "no memory".

**Possible causes.** `maxmemory` reached with an `allkeys-lru`/`allkeys-lfu` policy on an instance that also holds sessions, rate limiters, or a queue. A large value written (a 50MB blob, a runaway `$push` array) consumed the headroom. A keyspace scan job (`KEYS`, or an unbounded `SMEMBERS`) ballooned a value. Fork-based RDB snapshotting doubled the memory footprint at the worst moment. An application leak writing unbounded keys (no TTL, no maxmemory policy on the right instance). A cluster node rescheduled, so a third of the working set had to be re-warmed.

**Investigation.** `INFO memory` (`used_memory`, `used_memory_rss`, `mem_fragmentation_ratio`, `used_memory_peak`), `INFO stats` (`evicted_keys`, `expired_keys`, `keyspace_hits`), `CONFIG GET maxmemory maxmemory-policy`, and `MEMORY STATS` / `MEMORY USAGE <key>` to find the big keys. `redis-cli --bigkeys` and `--memkeys` (these are `SCAN`-based and safe on a live server). Check the persistence and replication child processes in `INFO persistence`.

**Tools.** Redis `INFO`, `MEMORY USAGE`, `--bigkeys`, Prometheus exporters for memory and evictions, keyspace notifications for large writes, and an alert at 70% of `maxmemory`.

**Fix.** Immediate: raise `maxmemory` or scale the node, and stop the leak (identify and bound the big value or the unbounded write). Short term: split instances by data class so the cache's eviction cannot touch sessions or limiters; set a `noeviction` policy on authoritative data so failures are loud; set `maxmemory-policy volatile-lru` only for keys that are all disposable; enable `lazyfree-lazy-evict` and `lazyfree-lazy-expire` so evictions do not block the main thread; add a size cap at the application layer (reject or externalize values over N KB). Long term: bound every key's lifetime and size, and use `volatile-ttl`/`allkeys-lfu` deliberately per instance.

**Prevention.** Per-instance budget and an alert at 70% and 85%. A "no unbounded key" rule in review, with `maxmemory` set per instance. Value-size assertions in tests. `maxmemory-policy` documented per instance in the deployment repo. Runbook for "we hit maxmemory" that starts with `--bigkeys`.

## 6.29.5 Connection pool exhaustion: everything hangs for 30 seconds

**Symptoms.** Requests hang for exactly 30 seconds and then fail with a timeout. No database error in the logs. Throughput collapses under modest load. A deploy that creates a client per request is often the trigger.

**Possible causes.** Pool size too small for the concurrency (a classic: 20 connections and 200 in-flight requests). A client created per request or per module reload, each with its own pool, until `max_connections` is hit. Connections leaked (a query created but never released, especially in a `finally`-less error path). A slow query holding connections for seconds, so the pool is a queue. A transaction held open across a network call. `statement_timeout` unset, so one pathological query occupies a connection indefinitely. In serverless, a new pool per warm invocation.

**Investigation.** `pg_stat_activity` grouped by `application_name` and `state` (`active`, `idle in transaction`, `idle`); count of connections by application; `SELECT now() - state_change` to see how long connections have been sitting. `pg_stat_database.numbackends` versus `max_connections`. `pg_stat_statements` for the slowest statements by total time. Check the application for pools per instance (`max: N` in the config) multiplied by instances.

**Tools.** `pg_stat_activity`, `pg_stat_statements`, `pg_stat_database`, `SHOW max_connections`, PgBouncer's `SHOW POOLS`/`SHOW CLIENTS`/`SHOW STATS`, APM span counts showing pool wait time, and per-instance pool metrics. Set a short `connectionTimeoutMillis` (2–3s) so exhaustion fails fast rather than hanging.

**Fix.** Cache the client as a singleton per process. Size the pool from measured concurrency and the database's capacity (a rule of thumb: `pool ≈ cores * 2 + effective_spindle_count` for a dedicated server; measure, do not guess). Put PgBouncer in transaction pooling mode in front if you have many instances. Set `statement_timeout` and `lock_timeout` so no query can hold a connection indefinitely. Fix the leak: every `pool.connect()` needs a `release()` in a `finally`. Shorten transactions and never include a network call in one. In serverless, one client per invocation or a pooled proxy.

**Prevention.** A synthetic test that opens more concurrent queries than the pool size and asserts fast failure. Pool-wait-time alerting. `idle in transaction` alerting (any connection idle in a transaction for more than a few seconds is a bug). A lint rule or wrapper that makes `pool.connect()` require a `finally`.

## 6.29.6 N+1 in an ORM: latency scales with page size

**Symptoms.** A list endpoint is fast with 10 rows and slow with 200. The database's total query count per request is in the hundreds. `findUnique`/`findOne` for the same id appears repeatedly in the logs.

**Possible causes.** A relation resolved lazily inside a loop or a template. `include` without a join strategy (Prisma without `relationLoadStrategy: "join"`, Sequelize `include` without `separate: true`, Mongoose `populate` inside a loop, TypeORM `relations` with lazy loading). Rendering a component that reads `order.customer` for each order in a list. A DataLoader created at module scope (so it caches across requests) or not created at all.

**Investigation.** Log the SQL with parameters for one request and count statements by shape. Turn on an APM database span histogram and look for a spike in a cheap statement. Count queries in a test and assert a constant (a great regression test: "rendering 200 orders issues at most 5 queries").

**Tools.** `EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)` on the repeated statement, `pg_stat_statements` with `calls`, Prisma query event logging, Mongoose debug mode, APM span grouping, and a test-level query counter.

**Fix.** In order of preference: (1) enable a join strategy so the relation comes back in one query (`relationLoadStrategy: "join"` in Prisma on Postgres, `include: [{ ..., separate: true }]` in Sequelize, an explicit `leftJoin` in Drizzle); (2) a split query (`WHERE id IN (...)` then stitch) which is often the least invasive; (3) a request-scoped DataLoader for the remaining lazy paths; (4) denormalize the display fields you need into the list query itself.

**Prevention.** A query-count assertion in the test for every list endpoint. A lint/review rule against property access on a relation inside a loop. Budget p99 per endpoint and alert on query count as a first-class metric. Preload relations in the repository layer so the N+1 has exactly one place it can happen, and test that place.

## 6.29.7 An index silently ignored

**Symptoms.** A query that is fast in staging is slow in production, or a slow query gets slower after adding an index. `EXPLAIN` shows a sequential scan or an unexpected index, sometimes only in production.

**Possible causes.** Type mismatch: the query compares a `text` column to a number, or a `timestamptz` to a string, so the index cannot be used (and the implicit cast can even make the predicate non-sargable). A function or expression wrapping the indexed column (`WHERE lower(email) = ...` without a functional index; `WHERE date(created_at) = ...` without one on the expression). A leading wildcard (`LIKE '%foo'`). Different collation between the column and the query. A stale plan after a statistics refresh or a parameter-type change. For MongoDB: an index whose field order does not match the query's equality/sort/range order, or a partial index whose filter is not a superset of the query's. An index present but hidden. In Elasticsearch: a field mapped `text` where the query used a `term`, or a sort on a field without doc values. An Oracle/SQL-Server-flavored answer: stale statistics, or a histogram that makes the optimizer overestimate.

**Investigation.** `EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, VERBOSE)` in production (a read-only statement, safe). Compare the *actual* rows examined with the rows returned. Compare `SHOW plan_cache` / prepared plan shape: a generic plan chosen for a parameter distribution that differs from production. For MongoDB: `explain('executionStats')` and check `IXSCAN` vs `COLLSCAN`, `keysExamined` versus `nReturned`, and whether a `SORT` stage appeared. For Elasticsearch: `profile: true` and check which clauses scored.

**Tools.** `EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)`, `pg_stat_statements`, `pg_stat_user_indexes` (an index with `idx_scan = 0` over a long period is a candidate for removal — and a hint that you are missing a *different* index), auto-explain in production, `ANALYZE`, and MongoDB's `explain` plus `db.collection.getIndexes()`.

**Fix.** Add the functional or expression index (`CREATE INDEX ON users (lower(email))`), fix the type mismatch in the query or the column, use `pg_trgm` for leading-wildcard search, reorder the MongoDB compound index to equality → sort → range, add a `keyword` subfield (or reindex) for a term query on a `text` field in Elasticsearch, and re-analyze after a bulk load.

**Prevention.** A migration check that every new index is used (`idx_scan` monitoring with an alert after 7 days). A staging load test with production-like data volume, because a sequential scan on 100 rows is the correct plan and a 5-second scan on 100M rows is not. A rule that every query shipped with a new index has an `EXPLAIN` in the PR. Watch for "unused index" and "missing index" both, via `pg_stat_user_indexes`.

## 6.29.8 Search relevance complaints: users say search "got worse"

**Symptoms.** Support tickets: "the search used to find X and now it returns nothing." Some queries work, some do not. Nothing in the code changed, or an index was rebuilt last week.

**Possible causes.** An analyzer mismatch: the index was built with a stemmer or a language analyzer and the query is not, so "running" never matches "run". A synonym set changed and the index was not rebuilt (or was rebuilt and the *query* analyzer now disagrees with it). A field that was a `keyword` and got remapped to `text` (or the reverse) in a reindex. A `text` field with `index_options` or `term_vector` set to `positions` only, so phrase queries degrade. A newly added field with an accidental mapping (a `text` where you query with `term`). A boost or `function_score` change shipped without a relevance evaluation. `index.max_result_window` silently truncating deep results, or a default `size` of 10 that used to be 50.

**Investigation.** Reproduce with the exact user query. Run the `_analyze` API on the query string and on a known-good document to see what terms each produces — a divergence here explains most tickets. Confirm the document is in the index at all (`_search` with `match_all` plus the tenant filter, and check `total.hits`). Compare mappings across environments with the `_mapping` API. Check the analyzers actually in use on the field (`_field_caps`). Then look at scoring with `explain: true` on the failing query and on a query that works.

**Tools.** `_analyze`, `_explain`, `_field_caps`, `_mapping`, `_search` with `profile: true`, the relevance-scoring tooling for A/B comparison, and a small labelled evaluation set with recall@k and NDCG.

**Fix.** Make the query analyzer match the index analyzer (or use `search_analyzer` on the field, which is the correct fix and is a reindex). Put changed synonyms in a search-time set instead of reindexing. Add the missing `keyword` multi-field. Restore the intended `size` and `boost`. Reindex if a mapping change requires it.

**Prevention.** A relevance evaluation suite in CI: a labelled set of queries with expected top-k, run on every index build and every analyzer change. Never change analyzers or synonyms without it. An index template in version control with a mapping diff check in the deploy pipeline. Log zero-result queries with the analyzed terms, so "search found nothing" becomes a data set rather than a ticket.

## 6.29.9 Time-series cardinality explosion: Prometheus OOMs

**Symptoms.** The Prometheus process is killed by the OOM killer, restarts, and drops data. Scrapes fail with "out of samples". Dashboard graphs have gaps. Head series count is in the tens of millions.

**Possible causes.** A high-cardinality label was introduced: `user_id`, `request_id`, `trace_id`, `error_message`, a full URL with an id, an `email`, a raw SQL statement, a Kubernetes pod name where `deployment` would do, or a `version` label that changes on every deploy. A fleet increase (more scrape targets) with no reduction in labels. A metric that used to be a `Summary` is now a `Histogram` with many buckets, or a histogram's `le` labels were split by another label. A `label_replace` or a recording-rule change that multiplies series. `-Inf`/`+Inf` buckets plus a `quantile` label.

**Investigation.** `prometheus_tsdb_head_series`, `prometheus_tsdb_head_chunks`, and the WAL metrics. Query the TSDB status API for the series count per metric name: `count by (__name__)({__name__=~".+"})` is expensive, so use the `/api/v1/status/tsdb` endpoint or a `topk` on cardinality, or run `promtool tsdb analyze` on a block offline to find the highest-cardinality label sets. Look for label values with a very high distinct count and a name that suggests uniqueness. Check the scrape target count and the `scrape_samples_scraped` metric.

**Tools.** `promtool tsdb analyze` (label-pair cardinality report), the TSDB status API, the Prometheus UI's "Status → TSDB" screen, `grafana` dashboards of series-per-metric, alerting on `increase(prometheus_tsdb_head_series_total[1h])` and on the rate of new label values, and recording rules that pre-aggregate the worst offenders.

**Fix.** Remove the label at the source (instrumentation change, then a short-lived recording rule or a relabel rule to drop it). For an immediate relief, use `metric_relabel_configs` to drop the label before ingestion (loses the dimension entirely, which is usually right for a unique id). Reduce buckets. Reduce scrape targets via aggregation. Then add recording rules to pre-aggregate the expensive queries and downsampling for long-range retention. Restart the process to reclaim memory (it is safe; the WAL replays).

**Prevention.** A label allow-list enforced in CI (a linter over the code, or a `promtool` check plus a unit test that asserts the label names of a new metric). Series-count alerts with a per-metric breakdown. A cardinality budget per metric in the instrumentation guide. Historical sharding (one Prometheus per team, or Thanos/Mimir/Cortex with remote write) so one team cannot OOM everyone. Review every new metric in the same PR as the code that emits it.

## 6.29.10 A migration blocked on a lock

**Symptoms.** A deploy pipeline hangs on `ALTER TABLE`. On the primary, everything is slow, and `pg_stat_activity` shows dozens of `SELECT` queries with `wait_event_type = 'Lock'` and `state = 'idle in transaction'` for one session. Users report timeouts across the whole application, not just one endpoint.

**Possible causes.** An `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` statement queued behind a long-running transaction that has not committed. A `CREATE INDEX` (non-concurrent) waiting for writes. A `SET NOT NULL` or `ADD FOREIGN KEY` doing a full scan while holding the lock. A migration that ran outside a `lock_timeout`, so it waits forever, and *every* subsequent query queues behind it (this is the amplification: one blocked DDL blocks the entire table).

**Investigation.** `SELECT pid, state, wait_event_type, wait_event, now() - xact_start AS xact_age, query FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE wait_event_type = 'Lock'`. Then find the *blocker*: the `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` lock holder is the session with an older `xact_start` that is `idle in transaction` (or running a long query). `pg_blocking_pids(pid)` gives it directly. Check whether the blocker is a long ETL job, a replication slot consumer, a monitoring agent, or a leaked interactive session.

**Tools.** `pg_stat_activity` and `pg_blocking_pids`, `pg_locks`, `lock_timeout`/`statement_timeout` set per session, `pg_terminate_backend` (as a last resort — killing the blocker rolls back its transaction, which may itself be a long rollback), a terminal dashboard of idle-in-transaction sessions, and statement timeouts at the application level.

**Fix.** Cancel the queued DDL (it is not holding anything, it is only queued). Commit or terminate the blocking session; find and fix the long transaction (usually an app bug: a transaction left open across a request, or a `pg_sleep` in a test). Re-run the migration with `SET lock_timeout = '3s'` so it fails fast, and retry on a schedule until it wins the race. Use the non-blocking variants permanently: `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY`, `NOT VALID` + `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` for foreign keys and `NOT NULL`, and split "add column" from "set default" when the default is volatile.

**Prevention.** `lock_timeout` in every migration session, enforced by a migration wrapper or a CI check. An alert on any `idle in transaction` older than 30 seconds and on any DDL waiting on a lock. Long transactions are the root cause, so cap transaction duration in the application and use `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` on the server. Migrations on large tables go through the "safe DDL" checklist in 6.24.8 as a code-review item. A staging rehearsal of the migration against a production-sized copy, which catches the long-transaction blocker more often than not.

**Bonus scenarios worth being ready for** (same format, shorter):
- **A consumer group falling behind** in a stream: lag grows, the retention window approaches, and a restart loses messages. Symptom: a `pending` count that climbs. Fix: scale consumers (bounded by partitions), optimize the handler, or move to a bulk path. Prevent: lag alerting at 50% of retention, and idempotent handlers.
- **A dual-write inconsistency** between two stores: a record exists in one and not the other. Fix: an outbox written in the same transaction as the source, a relay that publishes it, and a reconciliation job comparing counts and checksums. This is the answer to "how do you migrate data between two stores without downtime".
- **A read replica serving stale reads after a failover**: a monotonically non-monotonic read (a user sees an older value). Fix: sticky sessions or a read-your-writes check on the previous primary; prevent with causal sessions or a linearizable read for the writer's own reads.

---

# 6.30 Rapid-Fire
Advanced · **Must-know**

| # | Question | Key points |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Is partition tolerance optional? | No; partitions are routine, so every distributed system is P |
| 2 | What do you actually choose under CAP? | Behaviour per operation during a partition, and the freshness/availability trade |
| 3 | PACELC adds what? | Latency vs consistency in the *healthy* case, which is where you live 99.9% of the time |
| 4 | Linearizable read costs? | A leader or quorum round trip, and 2x the RCU in DynamoDB |
| 5 | Linearizable reads allowed in a MongoDB transaction? | No |
| 6 | What is "eventual" without a bound? | Meaningless; state a convergence SLO and monitor it |
| 7 | Phi accrual detector? | Reports a suspicion level from the distribution of heartbeat intervals |
| 8 | Why must a lease be fenced? | A failure detector can be wrong; a paused client can act after its lease expired |
| 9 | Redlock's known weakness? | Process pauses between acquire and act, so a stale holder can still write |
| 10 | Lock release must check what? | The token, in one atomic Lua call, in a `finally` |
| 11 | `MULTI/EXEC` is ACID? | No; no rollback, no isolation, only no interleaving |
| 12 | `KEYS *` alternative? | `SCAN` with `COUNT`, plus `SSCAN`/`HSCAN`/`ZSCAN` |
| 13 | `MOVED` vs `ASK`? | Permanent relocation vs in-progress migration; never cache `ASK` |
| 14 | `CROSSSLOT` fix? | Hash tags so the keys share a slot: `{user:42}:a` and `{user:42}:b` |
| 15 | `maxmemory-policy` for a limiter? | `noeviction`, so keys are never silently dropped |
| 16 | Why separate limiter and cache instances? | Eviction and growth in one would corrupt the other |
| 17 | RDB vs AOF `everysec`? | Snapshot loses everything since it; AOF loses about a second |
| 18 | `WAIT n timeout`? | Turns async replication into a tunable quorum at the cost of latency |
| 19 | Redis value size guidance? | Keep small; put bulk in object storage |
| 20 | Cache-aside's weakness? | A miss storm on expiry, so add jitter, single-flight, and SWR |
| 21 | TTL jitter size? | About ±20% |
| 22 | Write-around for? | Data unlikely to be re-read, to avoid polluting the cache |
| 23 | Write-back's risk? | Data loss if the process dies before the flush |
| 24 | Pub/Sub as invalidation? | Fire-and-forget; fine as an optimization on top of a TTL, never alone |
| 25 | Fixed window's flaw? | 2x the limit across a boundary; two requests in the same millisecond |
| 26 | Sliding log's cost? | One zset entry per request in the window |
| 27 | Sliding log member id must be unique? | Yes, or same-millisecond writes overwrite each other |
| 28 | Sliding window counter estimate? | `current + previous * (1 - fractionElapsed)` |
| 29 | Token bucket's burst size? | The capacity |
| 30 | Leaky bucket protects what a token bucket does not? | A downstream that cannot absorb bursts at all |
| 31 | Redis Lua float gotcha? | Lua numbers truncate to integers in replies; return `tostring` |
| 32 | Pass the clock in from the app? | Yes, so you control skew and can test |
| 33 | DynamoDB `Query` requires? | An equality on the partition key |
| 34 | `Scan` cost with a filter? | Unchanged: the filter runs after the read |
| 35 | Hot partition hard limit? | 1,000 WCU and 1,000 RCU per second per partition |
| 36 | Unique index rule when sharded? | It must be prefixed by the shard key |
| 37 | LSI vs GSI on consistency? | Both eventually consistent reads only |
| 38 | Sparse GSI purpose? | Keep a GSI containing only the entities that belong in it |
| 39 | `ConsistentRead: false` right after a write? | Can return nothing for that key; retry |
| 40 | Strongly consistent DynamoDB read cost? | 2x the read capacity units |
| 41 | Idempotent DynamoDB write? | A unique idempotency key attribute plus a condition on it |
| 42 | DAX does not do what? | Strongly consistent reads, and it does not speed up writes |
| 43 | Cassandra clustering prefix rule? | Range predicates must follow the clustering order |
| 44 | `ALLOW FILTERING` cost? | Reads and buffers the entire partition |
| 45 | `QUORUM` for N=5? | 3 |
| 46 | The anti-entropy rule? | `R + W > N` |
| 47 | `W > N/2` prevents? | Acknowledged writes that can be "unwritten" by a stale coordinator |
| 48 | `gc_grace` default? | 10 days; bounds hinted handoff and tombstone retention |
| 49 | LWT cost and limit? | About two round trips, and single-partition only |
| 50 | Compaction for time-series? | TWCS, with TTLs and time-windowed buckets |
| 51 | Cassandra write path order? | Client → coordinator → memtable → SSTable, with a row cache and bloom filter |
| 52 | Graph store: when? | Unbounded traversal depth plus relationships that carry data |
| 53 | Cypher traversal control? | Bound the depth, `LIMIT` early, filter inside the relationship pattern |
| 54 | Hyperedge in a property graph? | An intermediate node; edges join exactly two nodes |
| 55 | `DETACH DELETE`? | Deletes incident edges too; without it you get orphans |
| 56 | Hypertable buys you? | Automatic time chunking, compression, cheap retention drops |
| 57 | `end_offset` on a continuous aggregate? | Leaves the newest buckets raw so late data lands correctly |
| 58 | `time_bucket_gapfill`? | Fills missing buckets so an outage is not drawn as zero |
| 59 | Prometheus series definition? | A unique label set; memory scales with the count |
| 60 | Five illegal labels? | `user_id`, `request_id`, `trace_id`, `error_message`, a URL with an id |
| 61 | Prometheus head block? | In memory/WAL, and the OOM risk; the checkpoint controls it |
| 62 | Recording rules vs downsampling? | Rules pre-aggregate cheap series; downsampling keeps long ranges affordable |
| 63 | HNSW knob? | `ef_search` (and `iterative_scan` for filtered recall), with `m` and `ef_construction` at build time |
| 64 | IVFFlat requires what at build time? | A representative dataset, so build after loading |
| 65 | pgvector operator for cosine? | `<=>` for distance, so similarity is `1 - distance` |
| 66 | Operator class must match? | Yes, or the index is silently unused |
| 67 | Quantize-then-rerank? | Cheap ANN candidate retrieval, then a precise rescore |
| 68 | Prisma client lifecycle? | One per process; the `globalThis` cache is only for dev |
| 69 | `migrate dev` vs `deploy`? | Dev creates and may reset; deploy only applies pending files |
| 70 | Prisma's biggest query-API gap? | No `GROUP BY`, `HAVING`, windows, CTEs, `LATERAL`, or `DISTINCT ON` |
| 71 | Prisma transaction timeout default? | 5s, with a 2s wait for the connection |
| 72 | The transaction escape bug? | Using the global client inside the `$transaction` callback |
| 73 | `Promise.all` inside a transaction? | No parallelism; everything queues on one connection |
| 74 | Prisma's serialization-failure code? | `P2034`; raw Postgres is SQLSTATE `40001` |
| 75 | Drizzle's defining trait? | SQL-first: the schema is the SQL, and `sql` templates escape the builder |
| 76 | TypeORM's `synchronize`? | Destructive schema sync; never against real data |
| 77 | Sequelize eager-load fix? | `separate: true`, or accept the N+1 |
| 78 | Sequelize `paranoid`? | Soft deletes by default |
| 79 | Mongoose `.lean()`? | Plain objects, no hydration, no getters, no middleware |
| 80 | Mongoose `populate` cost? | A second query per path, one per array in the worst case |
| 81 | Mongoose middleware gap? | `updateMany`/`deleteMany` do not fire document hooks |
| 82 | DataLoader scope? | Request-scoped; a module-level instance caches across users |
| 83 | DataLoader dispatch primitive? | A microtask after the current synchronous block |
| 84 | Elasticsearch NRT? | Refresh interval, default 1s; use `refresh=wait_for` for read-your-writes |
| 85 | `_source` vs doc values? | Original JSON vs a columnar on-disk copy for sort/aggregation |
| 86 | Doc values on a `text` field? | Not available |
| 87 | `dynamic: strict`? | Rejects unmapped fields; the correct default for a typed index |
| 88 | Mapping explosion fix? | `dynamic` templates, `flattened`, or an index per entity type |
| 89 | `ignore_above` gotcha? | Longer values are not indexed but stay in `_source`, silently breaking aggregations |
| 90 | Deep pagination recipe? | A PIT plus `search_after` with a unique sort tuple |
| 91 | `doc_count_error_upper_bound`? | The terms-agg shard skew bound; check it before trusting a facet |
| 92 | ILM phases? | hot, warm, cold, frozen, delete, with rollover/shrink/forcemerge actions |
| 93 | BM25 `k1` and `b`? | Term-frequency saturation and length normalisation |
| 94 | Filters belong in `filter` because? | No scoring and cacheable |
| 95 | Postgres FTS when? | A few million docs, filters, and transactional data in one place |
| 96 | Fact grain? | One sentence; "one row per order line per day" |
| 97 | SCD type 2? | New row with validity dates, so history is preserved |
| 98 | Why partition a fact table by date? | Pruning, cheap retention, small working set |
| 99 | ETL vs ELT? | Land raw, transform in the warehouse: cheaper compute, keeps raw |
| 100 | Exactly once? | At-least-once plus an idempotent sink, achieved by dedupe or merge |
| 101 | Event time vs processing time? | Event time, with a watermark and an allowed lateness |
| 102 | Backfill rule? | Idempotent, resumable, through the same code path as the incremental load |
| 103 | Multipart upload leak? | Aborted uploads are billed forever; add a lifecycle abort rule |
| 104 | Multipart ETag? | Not the MD5 of the object |
| 105 | Presigned URL risk? | A bearer credential; constrain the key prefix per user |
| 106 | Object store rename? | None; it is copy plus delete, and it is not atomic |
| 107 | Exclusion constraint for no-overlap? | `EXCLUDE USING gist (resource WITH =, tstzrange(a, b, '[)') WITH &&)` |
| 108 | Why `[)`? | Half-open intervals: adjacent bookings do not overlap |
| 109 | Prerequisite for a scalar in a GiST exclusion? | `CREATE EXTENSION btree_gist` |
| 110 | Add an exclusion constraint safely on a big table? | `NOT VALID`, then `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT` |
| 111 | Booking with 10 identical seats? | A counter with `WHERE available >= n` and `RETURNING`, not intervals |
| 112 | Confirming an expired hold? | A conditional update on `status = 'held' AND hold_expires_at > now()`; 0 rows means refund |
| 113 | Fan-out on write vs read? | Push for the following feed; pull for large fan-out authors |
| 114 | Follow-graph read? | A join table with a composite PK plus a reverse index; a recursive CTE for depth |
| 115 | Multi-tenant: leading index column? | `tenant_id`, always |
| 116 | Multi-tenant safety net? | Row-level security, `FORCE`d, with `SET LOCAL app.tenant_id` per transaction |
| 117 | Schema-per-tenant limit? | Migration cost grows with N; connection and catalogue overhead |
| 118 | Database-per-tenant limit? | N pools, N backups, N restores, N migration states |
| 119 | Audit capture: trigger vs CDC? | Complete and synchronous vs decoupled and eventually complete |
| 120 | Tamper-evident audit? | A hash chain with the head stored outside the database |
| 121 | A failed idempotency key? | Store the key with the result and return the stored result, never re-execute |
| 122 | Distributed tracing id as a store? | Yes: it makes "which request caused this write" answerable |

# 6.31 Final Readiness Checklist
Advanced · **Must-know**

**NoSQL decisions and consistency**
- [ ] I can name the six NoSQL families with a data unit, a query model, and a pain point for each
- [ ] I can explain why partition tolerance is not a choice, and give the PACELC framing
- [ ] I can distinguish linearizable, sequential, causal, monotonic, read-your-writes, and eventual, each with a concrete user-visible example
- [ ] I can say what "eventually consistent" costs in latency and capacity, and name a convergence target
- [ ] I can compute the quorum trade for `N = 3` and `N = 5` and explain `R + W > N`
- [ ] I can build a latency budget from a user tolerance and decompose it across hops
- [ ] I can describe a failure detector, including why a fixed timeout fails and what phi buys
- [ ] I can argue the "one Postgres plus extensions" case against a polyglot architecture
- [ ] I know the decision procedure: enumerate access patterns, identify correctness-critical data classes, then choose

**MongoDB**
- [ ] I can explain BSON's advantage over JSON and name three types JSON lacks cleanly
- [ ] I can apply the embed/reference checklist and name the unbounded-growth rule
- [ ] I can justify an extended reference (a snapshot field) and know when a snapshot goes stale
- [ ] I can state ESR and explain why sort precedes range
- [ ] I can explain the multikey limitation and why a partial index beats a sparse one for soft deletes
- [ ] I know when to use TTL, text, wildcard, and hidden indexes, and the caveats of each
- [ ] I can read `explain('executionStats')` and name the four numbers that matter
- [ ] I can write an aggregation pipeline with `$match` → `$project` → `$unwind` → `$group` → `$lookup` → `$facet` and place `$sort` deliberately
- [ ] I know the 100MB sort limit, its three fixes, and when `allowDiskUse` is the right one
- [ ] I can name what `$setWindowFields` replaces and what it requires
- [ ] I know when a multi-document transaction is required and what it costs
- [ ] I can explain change streams: oplog, resume tokens, pre-images, and the retention bound
- [ ] I can compare read concerns, write concerns, and read preferences and say which knob to use for what
- [ ] I can list eight MongoDB limits, including 16MB, 100MB, and the multikey rule
- [ ] I can pick a shard key, explain targeted vs scatter-gather, and say what `limit` really costs on 20 shards
- [ ] I can compare MongoDB with Postgres `jsonb` honestly, including the cross-reference to 6.1–6.17

**Redis and caching**
- [ ] I can pick the right Redis structure for a leaderboard, a set of tags, and a queue
- [ ] I understand single-threaded execution and can name five commands that will stall it
- [ ] I know when to pipeline and what pipelining does not fix
- [ ] I can state what `MULTI/EXEC` does and does not guarantee
- [ ] I can choose an RDB/AOF/persistence and replication configuration and defend it
- [ ] I can explain `MOVED`, `ASK`, and `CROSSSLOT` and the hash-tag fix
- [ ] I can list all `maxmemory` policies and pick per data class
- [ ] I can implement the six caching patterns and name the failure mode of each
- [ ] I can explain TTL jitter, the three stampede protections, and stale-while-revalidate
- [ ] I can write the acquire, extend, and release Lua for a Redis lock and explain the token
- [ ] I can state Redlock's weakness and write a fencing-token check
- [ ] I know when `pg_advisory_xact_lock` beats a Redis lock, and the pooled-connection trap
- [ ] I can implement and compare fixed window, sliding log, sliding counter, leaky bucket, and token bucket
- [ ] I can explain the sliding-counter estimator and its worst-case error
- [ ] I know the Redis Lua float-truncation gotcha and the `tostring` workaround
- [ ] I can give five reasons not to make Redis a source of truth
- [ ] I can place sessions in Redis and explain session fixation

**DynamoDB and Cassandra**
- [ ] I can design a key schema from access patterns, and explain single-table design
- [ ] I know what a `Query` can never do, and why `Scan` is a production bug
- [ ] I can diagnose and fix a hot partition four different ways
- [ ] I can compare LSI and GSI on cost, consistency, and use
- [ ] I know the cost and restrictions of `ConsistentRead: true`
- [ ] I can write a conditional update that prevents a lost update or a double spend
- [ ] I can compare provisioned and on-demand, and explain the burst charge
- [ ] I can explain what Streams and DAX do, and the two-copies problem
- [ ] I can state the Cassandra query model and the clustering-prefix rule
- [ ] I can compute consistency levels and explain read repair, hinted handoff, and `gc_grace`
- [ ] I know what an LWT costs and what it cannot do
- [ ] I can choose STCS, LCS, or TWCS and explain compaction's role
- [ ] I can list three workloads Cassandra fits and three it does not

**Graph, time-series, and vector**
- [ ] I can justify a graph store with unbounded depth plus edge data, and refuse it for a two-hop join
- [ ] I can model nodes, edges, and hyperedges, and bound a Cypher traversal
- [ ] I can write the recursive CTE alternative and say when it wins
- [ ] I can explain hypertables, compression, continuous aggregates, and `start_offset`/`end_offset`
- [ ] I can name five high-cardinality Prometheus labels and the memory consequence
- [ ] I can compare HNSW and IVFFlat on recall, build, memory, and tuning
- [ ] I can explain why filtered ANN loses recall and name four fixes
- [ ] I can measure recall@k against exact KNN before shipping an index

**ORMs and data access**
- [ ] I can explain Prisma's singleton rule and the `migrate dev` / `deploy` / `push` distinction
- [ ] I can name five things the Prisma query API cannot express
- [ ] I know that an interactive transaction pins one connection and every statement must use `tx`
- [ ] I can explain why `Promise.all` inside a transaction gives no parallelism
- [ ] I can compare Drizzle's SQL-first model with Prisma's generated client
- [ ] I know TypeORM's `synchronize` danger and Sequelize's `separate: true` fix
- [ ] I know Mongoose `lean` vs documents, `populate` cost, and the `updateMany` middleware gap
- [ ] I can explain the N+1 problem and its four fixes, including the module-level DataLoader leak
- [ ] I can whitelist an identifier and parameterise a value when building a dynamic query
- [ ] I can run the expand/contract migration pattern and name which DDL locks or rewrites
- [ ] I can add a `NOT NULL` or a foreign key to a huge table without downtime
- [ ] I can decide when a repository earns its keep and when it is indirection
- [ ] I can write deterministic, idempotent seed data

**Search, OLTP/OLAP, and storage**
- [ ] I can explain an inverted index and the analysis chain
- [ ] I know why the query analyzer and index analyzer must match
- [ ] I can decide `keyword` vs `text` and explain `ignore_above` and mapping explosion
- [ ] I can explain near-real-time search, doc values, and the PIT plus `search_after` recipe
- [ ] I can read a `terms` aggregation's error bound and use `composite` for deep facets
- [ ] I can state what BM25's `k1` and `b` do and when to reach for `function_score`
- [ ] I can list the ILM phases and when `shrink` and `forcemerge` are safe
- [ ] I can state a fact table's grain and explain SCD types 1 and 2
- [ ] I can choose batch, micro-batch, or streaming and explain idempotent sinks
- [ ] I can explain event time, watermarks, allowed lateness, and restating closed windows
- [ ] I know the multipart upload rules, the ETag caveat, and the presigned-URL prefix rule
- [ ] I can say which object-store operations are eventually consistent

**Data modeling**
- [ ] I can state a fact, the entities and keys, the cardinality, and the normalization line for an unfamiliar domain
- [ ] I can write the indexes for a query set, with equality-then-sort-then-range reasoning
- [ ] I can list every denormalized field with its freshness contract and its blast radius on error
- [ ] I can name the first thing that breaks at 100x and the sharding key
- [ ] I can implement no-overlap booking with a Postgres exclusion constraint, `btree_gist`, and a partial `WHERE`
- [ ] I can handle capacity-based inventory with a guarded counter and `RETURNING`
- [ ] I can implement multi-tenant isolation with a leading `tenant_id` index, tenant-scoped uniqueness, tenant-prefixed cache keys, and forced RLS
- [ ] I can design an append-only, tamper-evident audit log with a documented legal hold
- [ ] I can write a change-stream consumer with resume-token persistence and idempotent side effects
- [ ] I can state a deletion and retention policy that includes a GDPR-versus-statutory conflict and how you resolve it

<!-- # Complete Full Stack Interview Roa.md (outline) -->

# PHASE 6: Database Systems

### SQL Databases ✅
- ✅ Schema design, Indexing, Complex joins, ACID transactions, Stored procedures, Query optimization, Migration tools
- ➕ Normalization (1NF–BCNF) vs denormalization, Join types, Window functions, CTEs and recursive queries, Subqueries vs joins, Isolation levels and anomalies (dirty read, phantom read), Locking (optimistic vs pessimistic), Deadlocks, `EXPLAIN` and query plans, B-tree vs hash indexes, Composite and covering indexes, Views and materialized views, Triggers, Constraints, Connection pooling, PostgreSQL specifics (JSONB, full-text search, extensions), MySQL differences, Backups and point-in-time recovery

### NoSQL Databases ✅
- ✅ Document stores, Key-value stores, Graph databases, Sharding, Replication, Eventual consistency, Aggregation pipelines
- ➕ Wide-column stores (Cassandra, DynamoDB), CAP and PACELC, Schema design in MongoDB (embed vs reference), MongoDB indexes, Change streams, Transactions in NoSQL, SQL vs NoSQL decision framework, Time-series databases

### 🆕 ORMs and Data Access (🟡)
- Prisma, TypeORM, Sequelize, Drizzle, Mongoose, N+1 problem, Raw SQL vs ORM, Migrations and seeding, Repository pattern, Transactions in ORMs

### 🆕 Search and Specialized Stores (🔴)
- Elasticsearch/OpenSearch (inverted index, relevance), Vector databases (pgvector, Pinecone), Data warehouses, OLTP vs OLAP, ETL basics, Object storage

### Caching Layers (🔴) ✅
- ✅ Redis integration, In-memory cache, Cache invalidation, Eviction policies, Distributed locks, Session storage, Rate limiting
- ➕ Cache-aside, write-through, write-back, Cache stampede, TTL strategy, Redis data structures, Redis persistence (RDB/AOF), Redis Cluster, Pub/sub in Redis, Rate-limiting algorithms (token bucket, leaky bucket, sliding window; **canonical entry**), HTTP and CDN caching

### 🆕 Data Modeling Exercises (🟡)
- E-commerce, Social network, Booking system, Chat, Multi-tenant SaaS, Audit/versioning
